# PEG-LEG GREG — BOOK IV — ACT II: BEYOND THE DOOR

**Manuscript-only NotebookLM source. Chapters 331–388.**

---

# CHAPTER 331

## THE MARKER FIELD

Antonius paid three copper to spend half a day arguing with posts.

I accepted immediately.

Not because three copper was enormous.

Because three copper for half a day was better than one copper for two hours had been, and because the work involved leaving Carrow.

Both mattered.

He found me near the lower market just after first bell.

No note.

No messenger.

Antonius himself.

That was unusual enough that I stopped before he spoke.

He wore a brown coat with mud dried to one knee and carried a rolled survey sheet under one arm.

"Free today?"

"Depends."

"On?"

"Whether you're about to ask me to do something stupid."

"Moderately stupid."

"Pay?"

"Three copper."

"How stupid?"

"Outside city. Half day. Marking old boundary posts against a current line. Mostly walking and telling me when I am about to write down the wrong thing."

"Why me?"

"Because you notice when physical things disagree with paperwork."

That was flattering in a way I distrusted.

"Who else?"

"Me. Pell. Cart driver."

"Lifting?"

"No."

"Terrain?"

"Field road. Some ditch edge. One low slope."

"Return by?"

"Before dark unless the horse joins a religion."

Reasonable.

"Three copper cash?"

"Cash."

"No debt credit?"

"Cash."

"Done."

Antonius nodded.

No negotiation.

That bothered me for six seconds.

Then I remembered I had once missed his one-copper task because I was out of town.

Maybe the market had moved.

Maybe the task was worse.

Maybe he had simply decided my time was useful.

All acceptable possibilities.

I went home long enough to tell Cala where I was going, refill water, check both crutch tips, and move the oval case deeper into the shoulder bag so it would not knock around on the road.

The holdfast stayed home.

I looked at it once.

Field work.

Unknown old posts.

A magical fixed holdfast that had demonstrated adhesion to a wooden bench.

Tempting.

No.

Untested load.

Unknown surface behavior.

Unknown release reliability.

Carrying it because `field` sounded relevant was how collections became accidents.

Separate problems.

I left it.

Antonius waited beside a narrow two-wheel cart near South Gate.

Pell sat on the back rail with a slate on his knees.

I had met him once before.

Maybe twice.

Thin, pale beard, ears that seemed designed for hearing mistakes from far away.

He looked at my crutches.

Then at Antonius.

"He walking?"

I said, "Usually."

Pell ignored me.

Antonius said, "Yes."

"All of it?"

"No. Seat between stops if needed."

Pell grunted.

Good.

Not a personality problem yet.

The cart driver was a woman named Sera.

Not first-life Sera.

Carrow Sera.

Important distinction because memory enjoyed reusing names to confuse me.

She had a wide hat and no interest in conversation.

Strong start.

We left through South Gate.

The oval clicked once in the bag.

I did not inspect it.

The city wall fell behind us.

Fields began almost immediately.

Not endless farms.

Carrow's outside edge was messier than that.

Storage yards.

Kitchen plots.

Brick cuts.

Goat pens.

Then broader grain strips divided by drainage ditches and old stone lines.

The road stayed packed earth.

The cart wheels knew every rut personally.

I sat for the first stretch.

Not pride.

Math.

If the job paid for observation, wasting my shoulders before the observation began was stupid.

Pell unrolled the survey sheet.

"Current east boundary runs from canal tooth fourteen to hill stone three. Six registered intermediate markers."

I looked at the paper.

"Registered when?"

He glanced at me.

"Twelve years ago."

"Posts older?"

"Some."

"How much older?"

"Unknown."

Antonius said, "That is why we're going."

Good.

Pell tapped the sheet.

"We are not deciding ownership. We are matching physical markers to this line and noting condition."

"So if a post disagrees?"

"We note disagreement."

"Not fix it."

"Correct."

I liked jobs with nouns that stayed in their lane.

The first marker was boring.

Excellent.

A waist-high stone post beside a ditch, square top, one face carved with a shallow hook mark.

Pell checked the sheet.

Antonius measured from the canal tooth using a cord marked at intervals.

I stood near the post and looked at it.

"What am I checking?"

Antonius said, "Everything we might regret not noticing later."

Too broad.

Pell said, "Position relative to road and ditch. Visible mark. Damage. Whether base looks moved. Anything nearby that makes the current reading questionable."

Better.

I circled the post.

No obvious fresh disturbance.

Lichen on three sides.

One side cleaner.

"South face cleaner."

Pell looked.

"Meaning?"

"Meaning south face cleaner."

He looked at me.

I smiled.

He wrote it down.

Hessa had ruined me in useful ways.

Second marker leaned.

Not much.

Maybe five degrees.

The ground around it had sunk on the ditch side.

Antonius measured.

Pell read.

"Within line tolerance."

I crouched as far as practical.

The base stone had an old crack.

Mud filled most of it.

One edge did not.

"This moved recently?"

Pell asked.

"I don't know."

"Then why ask?"

"Because one crack edge is clean."

Antonius crouched beside it.

Touched nothing.

Looked.

"Could be washout."

"Could be."

Pell wrote:

BASE CRACK / EAST EDGE CLEAN / POSSIBLE RECENT WASH

No one solved it.

Good.

Third marker was missing.

That improved the day.

The sheet said there should be a narrow iron-capped post twenty-three marked cord lengths north of the ditch bend.

There was no post.

There was a goat.

The goat seemed confident.

Pell measured twice.

Antonius checked the road bend.

Sera stayed with the horse.

I looked at the goat.

"Registered?"

Pell said, "No."

"Shame."

The goat ate something.

Maybe evidence.

We searched without searching dramatically.

No digging.

No grid.

Just walking the expected area and looking for iron, disturbed stone, old socket, fallen post.

I moved slower than they did.

That helped.

Not mystical compensation.

Just pace.

My crutch tip caught against something hard under grass.

I stopped.

Not post.

A flat stone edge.

I moved the grass aside with the crutch shaft.

Square cut.

About hand-width across.

Pell came over.

"Socket?"

"Maybe."

Antonius cleared grass with his boot.

A shallow square depression sat in the stone slab, filled with soil.

No iron post.

No broken cap.

Pell checked distance.

"Twenty-two and three quarters."

Antonius said, "Close enough to note."

I looked toward the goat.

"Questionable suspect."

The goat ignored due process.

We noted socket, no post, no conclusion.

Then moved on.

The fourth marker was where the work changed.

The road climbed a low slope beside a stand of pale grass.

At the top sat two posts.

Not one.

One stone.

One wood.

Both marked.

Both old.

Both within ten feet of the expected line.

Pell swore quietly.

Antonius said, "There it is."

"You expected this?" I asked.

"I expected something."

"Useful."

"The parcel changed hands twice in six years. The filed line stayed the same. Two neighbors now disagree which post is controlling."

There was the real job.

Not ownership.

Not adjudication.

Physical record before somebody turned memory into fact.

Pell pointed at the stone post.

"Registered description says gray stone, waist high, split crown, two-cut face."

The stone post was gray.

Waist high.

Split crown.

Two-cut face.

Easy.

The wooden post was dark oak, chest high, iron collar near base, with the same two-cut mark burned into one side.

Less easy.

"Wood newer," I said.

Pell gave me a look.

"Probably."

"Not enough."

I moved closer.

The stone post's two-cut mark was weathered almost smooth.

The wooden one was darker in the cuts but not fresh.

Antonius measured from the third expected point.

"Stone is four feet west of filed line. Wood is one foot east."

Pell said, "Filed tolerance is two."

So stone visually matched description but sat too far west.

Wood sat within tolerance but did not match material description.

Excellent.

I hated it.

We spent the next hour proving how little we knew.

Stone base had lichen down to soil.

No obvious fresh move.

Wood base showed no recent tool marks.

Iron collar older than I expected.

Maybe not six years old.

Could have been ten.

Could have been twenty.

I was not dating iron from vibes.

Pell found a second burn mark on the back of the wood.

Not the same two cuts.

Three short lines under a half circle.

I knew that mark.

Not artifact mark.

Survey crew family.

Old Greg had hired line crews before.

Road work.

Camp boundaries.

Claims after monster clearing.

The half-circle over three lines meant temporary carry-forward marker.

I thought.

Maybe.

Memory was strong enough that I felt confident.

Which meant I slowed down.

"I recognize that mark family."

Pell looked immediately.

"From where?"

Bad question.

For me.

"Old work."

"What does it mean?"

"I think temporary continuation or carry-forward. Not permanent boundary."

Antonius said, "Think?"

"Yes."

"How sure?"

I looked again.

Three lines.

Half circle.

Not closed.

One line shorter.

First life memory: a surveyor cursing because a crew had left carry-forward stakes in place after the final stones were set.

A younger me asking why anyone cared if the final line was obvious.

The surveyor saying because twenty years later nothing was obvious.

Apparently he had been right.

Annoying dead people.

"Moderately sure on category. Not sure this exact mark means the same locally."

Pell stared at me.

"Locally?"

"Marks travel. Meanings don't always."

He looked at Antonius.

Antonius said, "Note recognition as unverified."

Pell wrote:

GREG REPORTS BACK MARK RESEMBLES OLD TEMPORARY/CARRY-FORWARD SURVEY FAMILY / LOCAL MEANING UNVERIFIED

Perfect.

No one promoted me to surveyor.

We kept looking.

The wooden post had an iron collar.

Not decorative.

Set low.

Two narrow holes opposite each other.

Pell said, "Tie points?"

Antonius shrugged.

I crouched.

The ground on the east side had a shallow groove leading away through grass.

Not recent.

Old depression.

Like something had been dragged repeatedly or a cord had cut soil under tension.

Maybe livestock.

Maybe survey line.

Maybe water.

I said, "There is a groove east."

Pell wrote it.

We followed the groove twenty feet.

It vanished near a low buried stone.

That stone had no visible mark.

Antonius measured.

It sat almost exactly on the filed line.

Now we had three physical things.

Excellent.

The day was becoming professional hatred.

Pell wiped his face.

"Could be anchor."

Antonius said, "Could be field stone."

I said, "Could be where somebody tied a goat."

The goat was no longer visible.

Suspicious.

Sera called from the cart, "You done making the land worse?"

"Not yet," Antonius called.

We ate standing near the road.

Bread.

Cheese.

Water.

Mine from home.

No one bought lunch.

The oval stayed quiet.

Good object.

Pell kept looking at the wooden post while eating.

"If that back mark is temporary, wood could've been a working post set before final stone."

"Could," I said.

"Then why is it closer to filed line?"

"Because temporary crew might have been closer than whoever set the stone."

"Or the stone moved."

"Yes."

"But it doesn't look moved."

"Now."

He frowned.

Antonius said, "This is why we record before arguing."

Good Antonius.

After lunch we did something better than speculation.

We searched for corroboration.

Not the whole field.

Specific things.

If the wooden post was a temporary carry-forward, there might have been another similar temporary marker farther along the same line family.

The fifth registered marker sat beyond the slope near a dry drainage cut.

We walked there.

Stone post.

Correct description.

Within tolerance.

No wooden duplicate.

But thirty feet behind it, near a hedge, stood a cut-off oak stub with an iron collar rusted almost through.

Pell stopped.

"Well."

I said nothing.

We cleared grass around the back face.

Three short lines under a half circle.

Same family.

Closer.

Maybe exact.

Pell looked at me.

"Temporary."

"Still not proven."

"Come on."

"Two examples make a pattern. They don't write the manual."

He looked deeply offended by caution.

Hessa would have enjoyed him.

Antonius measured positions.

The oak stub sat just east of filed line.

The final stone sat nearly on it.

That supported temporary working post later replaced by stone.

At marker four, the wooden post might have been the same thing, except final stone ended up west.

Why?

Moved stone.

Bad final placement.

Changed local reference.

Filed line error.

Later replacement pretending to be original.

Too many possibilities.

We recorded all of them as possibilities by recording none of them as conclusions.

Good work was often disappointingly grammatical.

Marker six was ordinary again.

I appreciated it more.

By then my shoulders were tired.

Not failing.

Tired.

Antonius noticed before I said anything.

"Cart to last check."

I nodded.

No pride problem.

I sat.

Pell climbed up beside me.

"You actually know those marks?"

"Some family of them."

"From survey work?"

"Adjacent."

"What does that mean?"

"It means I have made enough bad decisions around people with measuring cords to remember what they yelled about."

He laughed once.

Progress.

The final check was not a marker.

Antonius wanted a sightline from hill stone three back toward canal tooth fourteen to note whether the filed route crossed an old drainage berm.

It did.

Barely.

The berm curved.

The filed line cut the inside shoulder.

Pell said, "That could explain west stone."

Antonius said, "How?"

"Crew avoiding soft berm."

"Then why file straight?"

"Because filed line is straight."

"And physical marker isn't."

"Exactly."

I said, "That explains a reason. Not whether it happened."

Pell looked at me.

"You are irritating."

"Professionally."

Antonius smiled.

We finished before fourth bell.

No horse religion.

On the ride back, I looked at the fields differently.

Not romantically.

Lines everywhere.

Fences.

Ditches.

Paths.

Old stone.

New wood.

People loved turning invisible agreements into physical objects and then pretending the objects could not lie.

Old Greg had owned land.

Lost land.

Taken land.

Paid people to tell him where land ended.

At S-class, boundaries had often been political problems wearing geography as a costume.

Here, four feet of stone mattered because somebody might spend the next ten years paying for the mistake.

Scale changed.

Human behavior did not.

The oval clicked at South Gate.

I felt it in the bag.

Ignored it.

We crossed into Carrow.

Antonius paid me near the market.

Three copper.

Cash.

He counted them into my hand.

I counted once.

Not because I distrusted him.

Because money remained money.

Five copper total now.

That was meaningful.

Not dramatic.

Meaningful.

Pell rolled the survey sheet tighter.

"If they ask again, you available?"

Antonius answered before I could.

"Depends on the task."

I looked at him.

He looked at me.

"Good answer," I said.

"I learned."

No standing job.

Good.

Pell said, "You should learn the local survey marks."

That was more dangerous.

Not because it was a job offer.

Because he was right.

"Maybe."

"I have an old reference sheet."

"Reference, not apprenticeship?"

"Gods, no."

"Excellent."

"I'll copy the mark page if I remember."

"If you remember."

"Correct."

Boundaries.

Beautiful.

I walked home with five copper and sore shoulders.

The temporary black conduit still ran along the front hall wall.

The red keep-clear line was intact.

Nobody had died while I was gone.

Strong building.

Upstairs, I put the three new copper beside the two old ones.

Five.

The number did something to the room.

Not literally.

The room remained small.

The high shelf remained high.

The holdfast remained wrapped.

The basket of mostly garbage remained mostly garbage.

But five changed options.

Mara's mark read no longer consumed everything.

Housing read no longer consumed everything.

A one-copper purchase no longer cut me in half.

Fourteen was still far.

Good.

Far was allowed.

I took out the narrow scrap.

FIELD LINE HALF DAY 3 C

I paused.

Then wrote:

5 C TOTAL

Under that:

TEMP / CARRY-FORWARD SURVEY MARK FAMILY ?

2 EXAMPLES / LOCAL MEANING NOT VERIFIED

STONE MARKER 4 FT WEST OF FILED / WOOD 1 FT EAST

DO NOT SOLVE FROM ONE MATCHING DESCRIPTION

I stared at the last line.

Too broad.

I rubbed it out.

Hessa had infected me enough already.

Then I looked at Mara's old rates written above.

MARK READ 2 C.

HOUSING 3 C.

OPEN 4 C + MATERIALS.

Five copper.

For the first time, I could pay for information and still have money afterward.

That mattered.

I looked up at the holdfast.

Then at the oval hanging near the doorway.

Two unknown pieces.

One useful enough to carry.

One still wrapped because I had never tested beyond the seller's bench.

I did not immediately grab either.

That mattered too.

Five copper was not permission to spend.

It was permission to choose.

Downstairs, Tavi shouted something about a spoon.

Lyssa shouted back that spoons did not need names.

Tavi disagreed.

The oval turned once on its cord when the upstairs doorway opened.

Click.

Normal.

I put the scrap away.

Tomorrow's problem could wait until tomorrow.

# CHAPTER 332

## THE HOUSING

Five copper was enough money to become irresponsible in more interesting ways.

I discovered this the next morning.

Not by spending it.

By realizing I could.

Two copper had made Mara's prices theoretical.

I could pay two for a mark read and have nothing left.

I could not pay three for a housing read at all.

Four plus materials had been a joke told by arithmetic.

Five changed that.

Not into affordability.

Into choice.

Which was worse.

I ate breakfast while thinking about the holdfast upstairs.

Cala noticed.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You're chewing like money."

I stopped chewing.

"That sentence means nothing."

"It means you're counting."

"I'm eating bread."

"Expensively."

Lyssa laughed into her cup.

Tavi looked at my bread.

"Can I have expensive bread?"

"No."

Cala gave him another piece.

Economic policy collapsed immediately.

The holdfast had cost two copper.

Fixed base.

Ring.

Seller demonstration.

Unknown safe load.

Unknown surface requirement.

Unknown release behavior.

Unknown service history.

Unknown almost everything useful.

Mara's housing read cost three copper.

Five minus three left two.

That was not a comfortable reserve.

It was a reserve.

The difference mattered.

I finished breakfast.

Then did not go to Mara.

I washed the cup.

Helped Tavi find a missing strip of cloth he had put inside a boot because apparently boots were storage now.

Checked the temporary front-hall conduit with my palm near the wall for three breaths like the linewright had said.

Normal.

Went upstairs.

Looked at the holdfast.

Still there.

Very patient object.

I took out Mara's checklist.

MARK.

MATERIAL.

MOVING PARTS.

STONE OR CORE.

VISIBLE DAMAGE.

KNOWN USE.

The holdfast had a mark.

Maybe.

I had not copied it carefully enough.

Material: metal housing, dark stone or core inside ring assembly.

Moving parts: ring definitely.

Maybe internal catch.

Visible damage: wear at base edge, old scratch near rear screw.

Known use: seller fixed it to a bench and made the ring hold under a pull I had watched.

That was enough to ask a better question.

Not enough to carry it across town carelessly.

I unwrapped it without turning the ring.

The tag still read:

DO NOT TURN RING.

Good tag.

I inspected only what was already visible.

No cleaning.

No oil.

No polishing.

The mark was on the underside of the base.

I had forgotten that.

Which meant seeing it required lifting the object.

Not activating it.

Still a load problem.

I used both hands while seated, raised it only enough to slide a folded cloth under one edge, then leaned the base against the cloth without touching the ring.

Slow.

Boring.

Alive.

The mark was shallow.

Two nested hooks facing opposite directions with a short vertical cut between them.

I copied it.

Then noticed something else.

The rear screw scratch was not a scratch.

Not exactly.

A narrow line circled half the screw head.

Tool wear.

Someone had opened the housing before.

Or tried.

That mattered.

I wrote:

REAR SCREW TOOL WEAR / UNKNOWN SERVICE

Then rewrapped it.

Now I went to Mara.

The holdfast did not fit comfortably in my shoulder bag.

Of course it didn't.

I wrapped it in the ordinary sailcloth and tied the bundle to the outside of the bag with two loops, base inward, ring protected against my side.

Then hated that.

If I fell, I could land on it.

If someone hit the bag, the ring could move.

If the tie slipped, two copper could become street noise.

I untied it.

Cala watched from the doorway.

"Going somewhere?"

"Eventually."

"With that?"

"Maybe."

"You have a basket."

I looked at the repair-sweep basket.

Of course.

The remaining mundane junk came out onto the floor cloth.

Eighteen pieces.

Still eighteen.

No spontaneous treasure multiplication.

I put the wrapped holdfast in the basket.

It sat low enough that the ring did not touch the sides.

I threaded a cloth loop through the basket handle and around my right wrist.

Then picked up both crutches.

Awkward.

Not impossible.

The basket hung from my forearm, not hand.

Weight pulled wrong.

I took three steps.

Stopped.

"No."

Cala said, "Good."

"You could have said that earlier."

"You were learning."

Everyone had become Hessa.

I needed transport.

Not a cart.

Not for one object.

A strap.

Something that let the basket sit against my back or side without occupying a hand or swinging into the crutch.

I had cloth.

Lyssa had opinions.

Dangerous combination.

She looked at the basket, then at me.

"Loose strap won't work."

"I was going to ask to borrow one."

"For the thing with the ring you're not supposed to turn."

"Correct."

"It'll swing."

I knew that.

I disliked hearing it.

She took one of the older household carrying slings from a peg downstairs.

Not one of the clean gray sheets.

A wide woven band used for bundles.

She folded it through the basket handles, crossed it underneath, then tied the ends high enough that the basket would sit flat against my lower back rather than my hip.

"Try."

I did.

Better.

Not good.

Better.

The basket touched my back but did not swing.

The holdfast stayed low.

I could use both crutches.

"If it shifts?" Lyssa asked.

"Stop."

"If the ring catches?"

"Stop."

"If you fall?"

"Complain."

"Greg."

"Protect head, don't twist toward basket, then complain."

"Better."

Tavi said, "Can I carry treasure?"

Three adults said, "No."

Excellent household coordination.

Mara's shop was open.

That felt suspiciously convenient until I remembered she had told me three mornings a week if she was there.

This was apparently one of them.

A man stood at the counter holding a brass plate.

Mara was not touching it.

"You want age," she said.

"Yes."

"I can tell you what the mark supports."

"Same thing."

"No."

"Close."

"No."

I waited outside.

Good specialist.

The man eventually paid something I did not hear and left with his brass plate looking disappointed by reality.

Mara saw me.

Then the basket.

"Questions first?"

"Questions first."

"Good."

I set the basket on the floor without crossing the threshold.

The oval clicked in my shoulder bag.

Normal.

Ignored.

"I have a fixed holdfast," I said. "Bought used. Seller demonstrated one pull on a bench. I have not turned the ring since. I copied the underside mark and noticed tool wear around a rear screw. I want to know what a housing read can actually tell me before I pay for one."

Mara looked at me.

"Better."

"Than what?"

"Most people."

"Dangerous praise."

"Don't enjoy it."

She pointed at the sign.

HOUSING 3 C.

"Housing read means I inspect accessible exterior housing, visible seams, fasteners, core seat if visible without opening, wear, likely service points, and whether the housing family tells me anything supportable."

"No opening."

"No opening."

"No function claim."

"Not unless housing itself supports one."

"No safe load."

"No."

"No promise it won't hurt me."

"No."

"No activation."

"Not for three."

"Would you turn the ring?"

"Not without a reason."

Good.

"If the mark is enough for a two-copper mark read, why pay three?"

"Because your screw wear may matter more than your mark."

There it was.

I looked at the basket.

Five copper.

Three spent.

Two left.

Still able to walk away with money.

Still unable to buy anything substantial afterward.

Still enough to make another decision.

I said, "Housing read."

Mara held out her hand.

I paid three copper.

It hurt.

Not survival hurt.

Good hurt.

Money becoming information.

I had two copper left.

Mara said, "Bring it in."

I crossed the threshold.

Click.

The oval pointed back toward the door.

I ignored it.

Mara cleared a section of counter.

"Set basket there. Don't lift the object until I tell you."

I set the basket down.

She untied the carrying sling herself.

Not because she distrusted my knots.

Probably because she distrusted everyone.

Correct profession.

"Show me how it's wrapped."

I did.

She watched my hands.

"Ring hasn't moved?"

"Not since purchase."

"You sure?"

"As sure as I can be."

"Seller moved it?"

"Yes."

"How?"

I described the demonstration exactly.

Bench.

Base fixed.

Ring turned.

Held under pull.

Released.

No invented force.

No invented duration.

Mara asked, "What was pulling?"

"Seller's hand through a strap."

"Body weight?"

"Some. Not full."

"Jerking?"

"No. Steady."

"How long?"

"A few breaths."

She nodded.

Then unwrapped the holdfast.

Did not touch the ring.

First she looked.

Longer than I had.

Then she used a narrow wooden wedge to lift one side of the base.

"Mark."

I showed her my copy first.

She compared.

"Copy's good enough."

"Meaning?"

"Meaning your hand works."

"Cruel."

She leaned closer to the actual mark.

Two opposing hooks.

Short vertical cut.

Her face changed slightly.

Not recognition.

Attention.

"Trade family?" I asked.

"Maybe."

Good.

She took out a small lens mounted in a square wooden frame.

Not magical-looking.

Probably magical anyway.

She held it over the mark.

No glow.

No dramatic revelation.

"Cut's older than the rear tool wear," she said.

"How much older?"

"Enough that I won't call them same service event."

Useful.

She moved to the rear screw.

The line around the head.

"Opened."

"Definitely?"

"Turned with a bad tool, at least."

"Could be tightened."

"Could."

"Could be loosened."

"Could."

"Could have failed."

"Everything could have failed."

Fair.

She inspected the seam around the housing.

There were four screws total.

Only one showed obvious tool wear.

That was strange.

"Why one?" I asked.

"Because somebody only needed one."

"For what?"

"That's your question, not my answer."

She took a thin strip of pale paper from a drawer and slid it along the housing seam.

It stopped near the worn screw.

She tried another point.

Paper went farther.

Back to worn screw.

Stopped.

"Housing distorted?"

"Slightly."

"From opening?"

"Maybe."

"Impact?"

"Maybe."

"Heat?"

"No obvious heat color."

Good.

She turned the base without turning the ring.

One corner had old wear.

Another had a small polished patch.

"Mounted often," she said.

"Seller called it fixed."

"Fixed doesn't mean permanent."

"Portable fixed point."

"Maybe."

That was more interesting.

A holdfast that had been repeatedly mounted, not built into one bench.

Still not a function beyond what I knew.

But it changed the object category slightly.

Mara inspected the underside again.

Then stopped.

"There."

A second mark.

Tiny.

Near the edge of the base.

Not cut like the trade mark.

Scratched by hand.

Three vertical strokes.

Then one diagonal through them.

A tally.

Maybe.

"Service mark?" I asked.

"Could be owner mark. Could be count. Could be somebody bored."

"Excellent."

"You paid for supportable."

"I know."

She looked at the ring.

Did not touch it.

The ring sat inside a dark collar.

The collar had a narrow gap around one side.

Mara used the lens.

"Core seat visible?"

"Barely."

"Material?"

"Dark stone. Maybe glass composite. Not enough exposed."

"Cracked?"

She shifted angle.

"I don't see a crack."

That was not `not cracked.`

Good.

"Anything else?"

She looked for another minute.

Then said, "This housing family was made to be serviced."

I waited.

"Supportable?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

She pointed at the screw pattern.

"Four exterior fasteners, separate collar seat, replaceable base plate. Not sealed manufacture. Somebody expected access."

"Common?"

"Not rare. Old working equipment does that."

"Does the mark tell you maker?"

"No."

"Period?"

"Broadly older than current shop standard. That's not useful enough to sell as a date."

"Trade?"

"Anchoring, rigging, restraint, load control. Something in that family."

That matched seller demonstration.

Visible value priced.

No miracle.

"Would you open it?"

Mara looked at me.

"Four copper plus materials."

"I know the sign."

"Then why ask?"

"Would you?"

She looked again at the worn screw.

"Not yet."

That mattered.

"Why?"

"Because one fastener has been worked badly and the housing is slightly distorted there. If I open it, I may turn a readable problem into a broken one."

"So don't."

"Correct."

"What would you do first?"

"Find out whether the ring behaves normally under a controlled load without opening the housing."

I stared at her.

"You said housing read doesn't activate."

"It doesn't."

"So that's separate."

"Yes."

"Price?"

"Depends on setup."

"Of course."

"Bench test with my fixtures, no guarantee, owner operates ring, I observe housing? Two copper if nothing special. More if I need another pair of hands or a load frame."

I had two copper.

Exactly.

There it was.

The old trap.

Exactly enough.

I looked at the holdfast.

Then at Mara.

"Today?"

"Could."

"For two."

"If simple fixture."

"Leaving me zero."

"Your purse is not part of my pricing."

"Rude."

"Useful."

I wanted to know.

Very badly.

Five copper had turned into the ability to ask one expensive question and still have another expensive question waiting behind it.

That was the real ladder.

Not buying everything.

Discovering that information itself had depth.

I could spend the remaining two.

Get a controlled load observation.

Maybe learn whether the worn screw mattered.

Maybe learn nothing.

Then leave with zero.

Or I could stop.

Take the housing read.

Keep two copper.

Earn again.

Return when two copper was not my entire remaining ability to move.

Mara waited.

No sales pressure.

Also good specialist.

"Not today," I said.

She nodded once.

No praise.

Even better.

"What did I buy for three?" I asked.

She looked annoyed.

"You were here."

"I want the summary."

That changed her expression.

Slightly.

"Fine. Housing family consistent with older serviceable working equipment in anchoring, rigging, restraint, or load-control use. Repeated mounting wear. One rear fastener worked with poor or mismatched tool after original housing mark was cut. Slight seam distortion at that fastener. No obvious exterior heat damage. No visible core-seat crack from accessible angle, which is not certification. Housing designed for service, but I would not open it before a controlled behavior check because the worked fastener may be part of a preexisting problem."

Perfect.

I wrote it down.

Not all of it.

Enough.

Then added:

DO NOT OPEN FIRST

CONTROLLED LOAD BEFORE SERVICE

BENCH OBSERVATION ~2 C IF SIMPLE / NOT TODAY

Mara watched.

"You missed one."

"What?"

"Repeated mounting wear means somebody considered it worth moving and setting again."

I looked at the base.

That was interpretation.

Supportable enough.

"Useful."

"Sometimes."

I added:

REPEATEDLY MOUNTED / WORKING TOOL, NOT ONE-BENCH FIXTURE ?

Question mark.

Mara approved by not objecting.

I rewrapped the holdfast exactly as before.

She stopped me before I tied the sling.

"Your basket is wrong."

"It got here."

"That is not the same thing."

"What's wrong?"

"Ring side needs a rigid guard if you're carrying it against your body. Cloth keeps dirt off. It does not stop pressure."

I looked at the basket.

"I didn't turn it."

"Good. Keep being lucky."

That was unpleasant.

"What would you use?"

She pointed at a scrap bin under the counter.

"Two thin slats across basket mouth. Tie them so the bundle can't rise into them. Ring below slats."

"Price?"

"For scrap wood?"

"Yes."

She stared.

"Go away."

Free advice.

Excellent return on three copper.

I found two discarded packing slats outside a grocer three doors down after asking.

Free.

Tied them across the basket mouth.

The holdfast sat below them.

Now if the basket pressed against my back, the slats took the pressure before the wrapped ring did.

Better.

Still awkward.

But less stupid.

I walked home with two copper.

The oval clicked at Mara's door when I left.

Normal.

Then at the building front door.

Normal.

Then upstairs.

Normal.

It did not tell me whether spending three copper had been smart.

Good.

That would have been intolerable.

Lyssa was at the table when I returned.

She looked at the basket.

Then the slats.

"Better."

"Mara insulted our transport."

"Mara was right."

"This household is hostile."

"What did she say?"

I gave her the short version.

Older serviceable working housing.

Repeated mounting.

One badly worked fastener.

Slight distortion.

Do not open first.

Controlled load next if I wanted more.

"How much?" she asked.

"Three."

She looked at me.

Not shocked.

Just recalculating.

"You have two left."

"Yes."

"And the next thing costs two."

"If simple."

"You're not doing it today."

"Correct."

"Good."

"Stop approving me."

"No."

I put the holdfast back on the high shelf.

Same orientation.

Ring sideways.

Tag visible.

DO NOT TURN RING.

Then I took out my narrow scrap.

Five copper had become two.

But I knew more.

Not what the holdfast truly was.

Not what it could safely hold.

Not whether the worn screw was harmless service damage or the beginning of failure.

I knew the next question.

That was worth something.

Maybe three copper.

Hopefully.

I looked at the remaining two coins.

The old version of me would have hated the smaller pile.

The newer version hated it too.

Growth was beautiful.

But the pile did not feel like failure.

It felt like runway I had chosen not to burn.

That was different.

I put the two copper away.

Upstairs, the oval hung quietly.

The holdfast sat wrapped beside its warning tag.

One artifact had taught me that useful did not mean trustworthy.

The other had just taught me that owning a question could be more expensive than owning the object.

I suspected this hobby was going to be terrible for my finances.

# CHAPTER 333

## THE FAR POINT

Hessa sent for me three days after Mara told me not to open the holdfast.

This was probably unrelated.

I still distrusted timing.

The message came folded around a piece of chalk.

Not wrapped.

Not tied.

Just paper folded twice with a short white stick inside.

Tavi shook it.

"Treasure?"

"No."

"How do you know?"

"It sounds educational."

He handed it over immediately.

Good instincts.

The note said:

GREG

MIDDAY

SAME ROOM

BRING BOTH CRUTCHES

NO OTHER EQUIPMENT

HESSA

I read the last line twice.

No other equipment.

The oval stayed home.

The holdfast stayed home.

The narrow scrap stayed in my bag because that was arguably stationery.

Then I took it out too.

Hessa had written no other equipment.

I had learned something about exact language.

Lyssa watched me remove the scrap.

"Scared?"

"Respectful."

"Same face."

"Hostile household."

I left with two crutches, clothes, and a deep sense that I was underprepared for sitting in a room.

The room was the same.

Table.

Chair.

Marks.

Nothing theatrical.

Hessa was already there.

She looked at my hands.

Then at my bagless shoulders.

"Good."

"I almost brought notes."

"Why didn't you?"

"You said no other equipment."

"Notes are not equipment."

I stared.

She stared back.

"You wanted me uncertain."

"No. I wanted you to decide whether language mattered."

"That is worse."

"Sit."

There she was.

I sat.

Both crutches leaned within reach.

Hessa moved the table away.

Not far.

Enough to clear the floor.

Then she placed one chalk mark near my right foot.

One at middle distance.

One farther than she had ever asked me to work.

Not dramatically far.

Maybe two arm spans beyond the middle mark.

Still inside the room.

Still visible.

Still far enough that I disliked it immediately.

"What are we doing?"

"Three positions."

"Near, middle, far."

"No."

"You enjoy that word."

"Near, middle, and farther."

I looked at the last mark.

"Important distinction."

"Yes."

Far had become a category in my notes.

Hessa apparently refused my categories.

Reasonable.

"Same object?"

"No object."

"External effect?"

"Eventually."

"Draw first?"

"Yes."

Good.

Something familiar.

I placed both hands on my thighs.

Hessa said, "Minimal draw."

I did.

One.

Small.

Supervised.

Clean enough.

Not exciting.

Hessa watched my shoulders more than my hands.

"Again."

Second minimal draw.

Also fine.

"Stop."

I stopped.

"What changed?"

"Nothing."

"Your right shoulder rose on the second."

I frowned.

"Crutch fatigue?"

"Maybe."

"From walking here?"

"Maybe."

"Helpful."

"You answer maybe when you don't know."

"I learned from specialists."

No reaction.

Cruel.

She had me shift the right crutch farther from the chair.

Then the left.

Not use them.

Just change where they sat in peripheral view.

"Minimal draw."

Third.

Clean.

"Again."

Fourth.

A tiny hitch at the end.

Not enough to lose it.

Enough that I felt the shape change.

Hessa said, "What happened?"

"Late correction."

"Why?"

"I felt it thin."

"Did it?"

I thought.

"Maybe not."

"Then why correct?"

"Habit."

There it was.

Not magic problem.

Operator problem.

Old Greg knew this.

That was the irritating part.

You could understand a principle for forty years and still build the wrong correction into a new body.

"Again," Hessa said.

Fifth minimal draw.

No correction.

It thinned.

Then stabilized.

Hessa nodded once.

"Good."

That was almost praise.

I hated how much I liked it.

Then deliberate shaping.

No external effect yet.

Near position.

Small rotation.

Not object rotation.

Internal shaping target aligned to marked right-hand region.

The same kind of work as before.

Attempt one of the day.

Clean.

Second.

Slight drift.

Third.

Clean enough.

Hessa moved me to middle.

Not physically.

Attention.

The marked region moved outward.

First middle attempt failed early.

No external effect.

Just shaping losing edge before completion.

Second held longer.

Third gave the faint edge lift feeling I had known before.

Hessa stopped me.

"What did you feel?"

"Possible lift."

"Possible?"

"I know."

"Good."

I looked at her.

"You like possible now?"

"I like accurate."

Fair.

Then she pointed to the farther chalk mark.

"Same shaping."

I looked at it.

"Without external effect?"

"Yes."

"Just position specification."

"Yes."

That was new enough.

I closed my eyes.

"Open."

I opened them.

"Why?"

"Because you will not always be allowed to remove the room."

Annoying.

True.

I set the target.

Not a physical thing.

Not a point I could touch.

A farther specified region.

Same operation.

Different distance.

First attempt collapsed immediately.

Not painful.

Not dangerous.

Just absent.

Second reached farther but smeared.

Third held a fraction of a breath and then vanished.

Hessa said, "Stop."

I exhaled.

"That's it?"

"For shaping."

"That sounded ominous."

She walked to the farther mark.

Then placed a small white bead on the floor.

Not a sleep bead.

Not game hardware.

Plain ceramic.

No visible mark.

"External effect?"

"Yes."

There it was.

My first supervised external-effect attempt in a while.

I sat straighter.

Hessa said, "Do not improve because you are excited."

"That feels targeted."

"It is."

The near aggregate had four clean in ten.

Middle one in six plus one possible edge lift.

Far zero in one.

Old totals in my head.

Thirty-six supervised minimal draws.

Thirty-two deliberate shaping.

Seventy-eight external effects.

Hessa had not asked me to track those aloud.

Good.

I knew anyway.

She pointed at the near chalk mark first.

"Move the bead one grain toward you."

The bead sat at far mark.

"That's farther."

"The bead is farther. The requested effect is near to itself."

I paused.

"You are separating my target distance from effect distance."

"Yes."

That mattered.

A lot.

My earlier categories had mixed too many things together.

How far away the target was.

How large the intended effect was.

Where in my own shaping process I felt control.

Different axes.

I hated useful lessons.

"One grain," Hessa said.

I began.

First external attempt of the day.

The bead did not move.

Second.

Nothing.

Third.

I thought it shifted.

Then realized I had changed my head angle.

"No," I said before Hessa could.

"Good."

Fourth.

A tiny scrape.

Maybe.

No.

I looked.

The bead sat against a chalk fleck that had not been touching it before.

Hessa crouched.

Did not touch.

"Again."

Fifth.

Nothing.

She marked the chalk beside the bead with her fingernail.

"That fourth was movement."

My chest tightened.

Not because it was large.

Because it was far.

Target far.

Effect tiny.

Different category.

"Clean?" I asked.

"No."

"Why?"

"You overcorrected after contact."

Of course.

"So external effect occurred but not clean."

"Yes."

Good.

Better than fake success.

She moved the bead back.

"Again."

Sixth.

Nothing.

Seventh.

Tiny movement.

No scrape this time.

One grain.

Maybe less.

Hessa watched.

Then said, "Clean."

I stared at the bead.

There it was.

Far target.

Tiny effect.

Clean.

Not power.

Control reaching farther than before.

My heart wanted to make it important.

Old Greg wanted to classify it.

New Greg wanted to do it again immediately.

Hessa said, "Stop."

Cruel woman.

"Why?"

"Because now you want the result."

"That seems normal."

"It is."

"Then?"

"And therefore useful to stop."

I leaned back.

"You train joy out of people."

"No. I train greed out of repetitions."

That was annoyingly good.

I looked at the bead.

"One clean far-target tiny effect."

"Yes."

"Can I write that down?"

"Later."

"You enjoy power."

"You are nineteen."

"Physically."

She looked at me.

I shut up.

Then she did something worse.

She moved the bead from the farther mark to the middle mark.

"Same one-grain effect."

"After stop?"

"New condition."

"That's cheating."

"Yes."

First middle-target attempt.

Clean.

Second.

No movement.

Third.

Clean enough.

Hessa said nothing.

Then near target.

First clean.

Second overstrong.

The bead moved three grains.

I grimaced.

"Too much."

"Yes."

Third near.

Clean.

She stopped the session.

I waited.

She did not summarize.

Of course.

"What are you testing?" I asked.

"You."

"Specifically."

"Whether your distance problem is one problem."

I looked at the three chalk positions.

"It isn't."

"Probably not."

Good word.

"Far shaping still weak."

"Yes."

"But far target with tiny external movement can succeed."

"Yes."

"So the target-position specification can reach farther than sustained shaping quality suggests."

"Maybe."

"And the effect magnitude matters separately."

"Yes."

"And my correction habit gets worse when I feel thinning."

"Yes."

"And you could have told me this in one sentence."

"No."

"You absolutely could."

"You would have believed the sentence instead of the result."

I hated her.

Professionally.

"Next time?"

"Not selected."

There it was.

No schedule.

No automatic progression.

No promise.

I stood carefully.

My right shoulder was tired from the walk and the sitting posture more than the magic.

Hessa noticed.

"Crutches."

I took both.

"Any restriction change?"

"No."

"Outside practice?"

"No."

"Independent tiny far-target bead movement?"

"No."

"Worth asking."

"No."

There she was.

I left.

The street felt louder after Hessa's room.

Not brighter.

Not magical.

Just less bounded.

I wanted my notes.

Badly.

That was probably why she had let me leave without them.

At home I went upstairs before anyone could ask how it went.

The oval hung beside the doorway.

The holdfast sat wrapped on the high shelf.

Neither had anything to do with the session.

Good.

I took out the narrow scrap.

Then realized I had left it home.

Of course.

So I used a fresh scrap.

I wrote:

HESSA

MINIMAL DRAWS: 5 TODAY

DELIBERATE SHAPING: NEAR 3 / MIDDLE 3 / FARTHER 3

EXTERNAL: FAR TARGET 7 / MIDDLE 3 / NEAR 3

Then I stopped.

That was already too compressed.

I added:

FAR TARGET != FAR EFFECT

ONE-GRAIN MOVE AT FAR TARGET CLEAN ON 7TH TODAY

EARLIER FAR SHAPING STILL WEAK

DISTANCE MAY BE MULTIPLE PROBLEMS

Then:

LATE CORRECTION HABIT WHEN FEELING THIN

DO NOT CHASE CLEAN RESULT

That last one was Hessa's lesson.

I resented giving her credit.

I counted totals.

Minimal draws had been thirty-six.

Five more made forty-one.

Deliberate shaping had been thirty-two.

Nine more made forty-one.

External effects had been seventy-eight.

Thirteen more made ninety-one.

I stared at that.

Forty-one.

Forty-one.

Ninety-one.

Round enough to tempt meaning.

Meaningless.

Good.

Near aggregate needed updating too.

But not cleanly from today's sequence because the old categories had mixed target position and effect type.

That was the whole problem.

I crossed out the heading I had started:

NEAR / MIDDLE / FAR

Then wrote:

OLD DISTANCE COUNTS MIXED AXES

KEEP FOR HISTORY / DO NOT PRETEND COMPARABLE

That felt better.

Not satisfying.

Better.

Lyssa came upstairs.

"How'd it go?"

"I moved a bead."

"Congratulations."

"Far away."

"Still congratulations."

"One grain."

"Tiny congratulations."

I looked at her.

"You are impossible."

"Did Hessa say good?"

"Once. Maybe twice."

"Huge day."

She was not wrong.

I folded the fresh scrap and put it with the old notes.

Two copper remained in the purse.

The holdfast still needed a future controlled test.

The oval still clicked at doors and sometimes did stranger things.

My own magic had just done something cleaner farther away than I expected.

None of those facts solved the others.

That mattered.

The world was getting more magical without becoming simpler.

I liked that better.

# CHAPTER 334

## HOUSE LEFT

Theatre looked different when nobody wanted me to move anything.

I distrusted it immediately.

The note had come that afternoon.

Not from Rinna.

Not from Nessa.

Marek.

That should have warned me.

It said:

GREG

TONIGHT

SECOND SHOW

HOUSE LEFT

COME WATCH SOMETHING YOU DID NOT BUILD

M

Under that, in smaller writing:

THIS IS NOT WORK

Then, squeezed into the bottom corner:

PROBABLY

I showed Lyssa.

She read it twice.

"You're going."

"I haven't decided."

"You're already looking for the clean shirt."

I looked at the chair.

The clean shirt was on it.

Traitorous furniture.

"That proves nothing."

"It proves you own one clean shirt."

"Cruel."

I went.

Not because Marek had written probably.

Despite that.

The public entrance had a line.

A real one.

Not West Glass chalk and poles.

People.

Couples.

Three children pretending not to push each other.

An old woman with a hat large enough to have its own weather.

Two men arguing quietly about whether the first show had been better last week.

I had not known the theatre had people who discussed it like that.

Of course it did.

That was the point.

I knew the rear hall.

The prop room.

The wardrobe turn.

The left-stage storage angle.

The cracked paint under one bench nobody fixed because the bench covered it.

I knew where Nessa kept dry cloth and where Marek absolutely did not return borrowed things.

I knew what the building looked like when the audience was not there.

Apparently that had tricked me into forgetting there was an audience.

A woman at the front table took bits from people ahead of me.

When I reached her, she looked down at a list.

"Greg?"

That was unsettling.

"Usually."

She found a line with her finger.

"House left, aisle."

"How much?"

"Marked."

"That is not a number."

She looked at me.

"Comp."

I hated that word.

"Why?"

"Ask whoever wrote your name."

Marek.

I hated the word more.

"Can I pay?"

"You can, but then I have to remove the comp, record the seat sale, and explain why the seat count changed after close."

"So no."

"I didn't say no."

"You made it expensive socially."

"That is theatre."

Good answer.

I accepted the seat.

House left aisle turned out to mean the outer seat in the third row from the back on the left side.

Not the best seat.

Not charity disguised as luxury.

A practical seat.

I could put both crutches against the wall-side end without making six people climb over them.

The aisle gave me room to shift my right leg.

Someone had thought about it.

That was worse than a free ticket.

I sat.

The oval clicked in my bag when I crossed the inner house door.

Normal.

Ignored.

The stage was smaller from the audience side.

That surprised me.

Backstage, everything had felt large because objects had to pass through it.

Frames.

Flats.

Bodies.

Crates.

Ropes.

People saying move while standing directly where movement needed to happen.

From the seat, all of that disappeared.

The stage became a rectangle of warm light.

The painted back wall looked like stone.

I knew it was canvas over a frame.

The window looked deep.

I knew there were six inches behind it before a brace.

A low table looked heavy.

Davin had complained about how light it was because the rear leg kicked sideways if you lifted the wrong end.

I had helped carry nothing in this exact set.

I still knew too much.

A boy two seats over whispered to his mother, "Is that a real wall?"

She whispered back, "Yes."

I nearly turned around.

Then stopped.

She was doing theatre correctly.

I was the problem.

The house lamps dimmed.

Not all at once.

Three rows of wall cups lowered in sequence.

A narrow brass strip beneath the stage lip took on a dull amber glow.

Not artifact in the dramatic sense.

Stage utility.

Probably a managed light rail or fed strip.

The audience did not care.

They were not supposed to.

The curtain opened.

Marek walked on carrying a basket.

The basket was empty.

I knew because he carried it one-handed by the wrong side and the rim did not pull.

Stop.

Audience.

Be audience.

He was playing a courier.

Of course he was.

His first line was:

"I have crossed three provinces, two bridges, and one marriage to bring you this letter."

The old woman with the enormous hat laughed before anyone else.

Good woman.

The scene moved quickly.

Marek was better than I expected.

That was unfair.

I had seen him backstage.

Backstage Marek borrowed nails and forgot where his belt was.

Onstage Marek knew exactly where his hands belonged.

He could lean against a table like he had been tired for ten years.

He could wait half a breath before a line and make the audience decide what he was thinking.

He could look at Hara across the room and make two hundred people understand that his character had just made a bad decision.

No explanation.

No note.

No tally.

Just timing.

I hated competence in new domains.

It was expensive to my opinions.

Hara entered through the painted wall.

Not literally.

A hidden door I had never noticed from the house.

I knew there was a side access behind the left flat.

I had walked behind it.

Still, when the wall opened with no visible seam and she stepped through wearing a dark blue coat, the audience made one collective sound.

Small.

Not a gasp.

Attention becoming physical.

That was interesting.

The scene was about a letter.

Then not about a letter.

Then apparently about whether Hara's character had betrayed someone named Els.

I had missed the first show in the run.

This mattered.

A lot.

The boy two seats over whispered, "She did."

His mother said, "Shh."

He was probably right.

Halfway through the first act, the stage changed.

Not scenery.

Sound.

Marek crossed to the window.

Hara stayed by the table.

He spoke quietly.

Too quietly for the back rows.

Except I heard him perfectly.

Not louder.

Closer.

His voice seemed to arrive from the air directly in front of us.

There.

Artifact work.

Or embedded theatre equipment.

Something.

I looked at the stage lip.

The amber brass strip was darker now.

Along the left wall, three small black disks had opened like flowers.

Sound cups.

Echo mouths.

Whisper plates.

Different places called them different things.

Old Greg had seen versions in council halls, military command tents, one extremely wealthy bathhouse, and a courtroom where the judge liked hearing himself too much.

Theatre use made sense.

Obvious value.

The audience paid to hear lines.

Of course a theatre with enough money would spend some of it making speech reach the cheap seats.

No hidden bargain.

No mystery.

Just magic doing work.

I smiled.

The boy's mother looked at me suspiciously.

I faced forward.

Marek said, quietly enough to sound private,

"I did not come back because I forgave you."

The room heard every word.

Hara did not answer.

That was better.

Then the amber strip brightened again.

The black disks closed.

The next line belonged to the room rather than the mechanism.

I knew, suddenly, why Nessa cared about where objects sat.

Not intellectually.

I had always known that.

But the work had an end product.

The exact angle of the table controlled whether Hara could cross behind Marek without blocking the audience's view.

The left-stage passage mattered because the hidden wall entrance had to stay invisible.

The flower frame route mattered because tonight, later in the act, the frame arrived onstage looking effortless.

Jori's complaint about a shelf corner became three seconds of clean motion nobody applauded because nobody noticed.

That might have been the purest form of competence.

Work disappearing into experience.

I had spent a lot of my second life thinking about jobs as money.

Fair.

Money had been urgent.

Then jobs as access.

Also fair.

Now I watched a hundred invisible bits of work become one visible thing.

That was different.

The first act ended with Hara tearing the letter in half.

The audience clapped.

I clapped.

The boy beside me said, "I knew she did it."

His mother said, "You said that before we knew what she did."

"I still knew."

Strong method.

During the interval, people stood.

I did not.

Not because I could not.

Because everyone else had decided the aisle belonged to standing bodies, and I had no desire to negotiate two crutches through a temporary human forest for no reason.

The old woman with the hat moved into the aisle and looked down at me.

"You know them?"

I looked at the stage.

"Some."

"You've been watching the side walls more than the actors."

Caught.

"I know some of the backstage."

"Dangerous."

"Apparently."

She nodded toward the black disks.

"New this season."

That got my attention.

"The sound cups?"

"Is that what they're called?"

"Maybe. Depends where they're from."

"They used to lose half the quiet lines back here."

"Better now?"

"Much."

There it was.

Market visible utility.

No mystery.

The woman adjusted her hat.

"Still hate Marek."

I blinked.

"Personally?"

"His character."

"Good clarification."

"He always plays men who need slapping."

"That may be typecasting."

She looked at me.

Then laughed.

"You do know him."

She went to find something to drink.

I stayed seated.

A minute later Nessa appeared at the side of the house.

Not onstage.

In the narrow wall passage beside the audience.

She saw me.

Pointed two fingers at her eyes.

Then at me.

I pointed at the stage.

She shook her head once.

No idea what that meant.

Then she vanished through a curtain.

The second act began.

This one had more movement.

A public room scene.

Six actors.

Two tables.

People entering and leaving from three directions.

A tray of cups.

The flower frame.

The hidden wall door.

A narrow raised platform at stage right.

And a small suspended glass lantern above center stage that I had not seen before.

The lantern held no flame.

It held a pale point of blue-white light.

Not bright.

Steady.

Artifact or stage device.

Again, obvious purpose.

When characters stood beneath it, their shadows sharpened.

When they moved beyond its center, the shadows softened.

That let the stage create a private-feeling pool inside a crowded room.

Clever.

Not powerful.

Useful.

Then I noticed the problem.

Not magical.

Human.

Marek entered too early.

Half a breath.

Maybe one full breath.

I knew because Hara had not cleared the flower frame yet.

From backstage, that would have been obvious.

From the audience, it looked like he had chosen to stop beside her.

Hara looked at him.

Marek looked at the frame.

The actor carrying it froze for less than a second.

Then Hara turned her line toward Marek instead of the man seated at the table.

"If you insist on arriving before you're wanted, at least make yourself useful."

The audience laughed.

Marek took the near side of the flower frame.

The other actor released it without looking surprised.

Together they moved it three steps.

Marek set it down.

Then answered with his actual line.

"I've never made that mistake successfully."

Bigger laugh.

The scene continued.

I sat very still.

There.

That was live recovery.

Not hiding error by pretending nothing happened.

Using the error as material.

Hara had not fixed Marek.

Marek had not apologized.

The frame carrier had not fought for the original blocking.

The whole thing had bent around the mistake and returned to shape.

I thought of Hessa.

Late correction.

My habit of feeling control thin and shoving harder.

Do not chase clean result.

Different domain.

Same temptation.

Not same rule.

Careful.

I was not going to turn theatre into magic homework.

Still.

There was something familiar in letting a bad moment remain small.

Marek had arrived early.

The scene had not died.

Nobody had dragged him backward to the mark and restarted reality.

Useful.

The rest of the second act went cleanly enough that I stopped tracking clean.

That was probably the point.

At the final scene, the blue-white lantern dimmed.

The sound cups opened again.

Hara stood alone by the fake window.

The set behind her went dark except for the painted window frame.

Then the glass panes showed rain.

Not water.

Light.

Thin silver lines falling over the glass from nowhere.

Illusion work.

Simple compared with combat phantasms.

Still beautiful.

The audience went silent.

Hara looked through fake rain at a street that did not exist.

Then said one quiet line.

"I kept the door because I thought leaving was the same as losing."

The sound cups carried it to the back.

The oval clicked in my bag.

I felt it.

My hand moved halfway toward the flap.

Stopped.

No.

Not now.

Not every click deserved me.

Onstage, Hara put her hand against the fake window.

The silver rain slowed.

Stopped.

Blackout.

The audience stood so fast I almost got hit by a coat.

Applause filled the room.

Real applause.

Not polite.

The old woman with the hat shouted, "Slap him next time!"

Marek, bowing onstage, heard her.

He pointed at Hara.

Hara slapped him.

Not hard.

Perfectly timed.

The house lost its mind.

I laughed until my right side hurt.

Not injured.

Just laughing.

Good problem.

After the bows, people filed out slowly.

I waited.

Crutches plus crowd.

No reason to race.

The boy beside me told his mother the rain was real.

She said, "Yes."

I approved this time.

When the aisle cleared, I stood.

The oval clicked again when I crossed the inner house door.

Normal.

Whatever the earlier click had been, it had not mattered enough to interrupt the play.

Good.

I was learning not to let objects train me.

Marek found me in the lobby still wearing half his costume.

Blue waistcoat.

No coat.

One boot unlaced.

There he was.

Backstage Marek restored.

"You came."

"Against my judgment."

"How was I?"

"Early."

His face changed.

"You saw that?"

"Flower frame."

He pointed at me accusingly.

"You are not allowed to watch theatre anymore."

Hara appeared behind him.

"He saw it?"

"Immediately."

She looked pleased.

"Good."

"Why good?" Marek asked.

"Because you were early."

"I recovered."

"You were still early."

"The audience laughed."

"At me fixing you."

"At us."

"No."

They were still arguing when Nessa arrived carrying the empty basket from Act One.

I pointed at it.

"Empty."

She looked down.

"Yes."

"Marek carried it wrong."

Marek said, "I carried a basket."

"Like it weighed nothing."

"It did weigh nothing."

Nessa closed her eyes.

Hara laughed.

"He's right."

Marek looked betrayed.

"Whose side is this?"

Nessa handed him the basket.

"Carry it like there are six bottles in it tomorrow."

"There are no bottles."

"Then act."

I liked Nessa.

Rinna came through the lobby with a small slate in one hand.

She saw me.

"Seat worked?"

There it was.

Not Marek.

Rinna had chosen the seat.

"Yes."

"Crutches clear of aisle?"

"Yes."

She marked something on the slate.

Not charity.

Operations.

Even better.

"Thank you," I said.

She looked up.

I meant the seat.

She understood.

"You moved three unpaid routes this month."

"I did not move three routes."

"You checked three routes."

"That is different."

"Then you checked enough for one seat."

"Is that accounting?"

"No. If it were accounting, you'd hate the number."

Probably true.

She moved on.

Marek said, "See? Not work."

I looked at him.

"You wrote probably."

"I believe in accuracy."

Hessa had infected the city.

I left before anyone gave me a task.

Outside, the night had gone cool.

People spilled from the theatre into the street still talking about the show.

Not about the hidden wall seam.

Not about the sound cups.

Not about the flower frame route.

They argued about whether Hara's character should have burned the letter.

Whether Marek's character deserved the slap.

Whether the rain at the end meant she was leaving.

The machinery had disappeared.

The work had disappeared.

Even the magic had mostly disappeared.

What remained was what people thought happened.

That felt important.

Not in a lesson way.

In a craft way.

Maybe that was why the theatre kept pulling me back.

It was a place where hundreds of practical decisions existed solely so nobody would think about them when they succeeded.

My whole life had been the opposite lately.

I noticed every coin.

Every threshold.

Every load.

Every failed draw.

Every fraction of movement.

Necessary.

But exhausting.

For two hours, I had watched a fake room become more real than the real room around it.

That was worth something.

Apparently exactly one comp seat.

I walked home with two copper still in my purse.

No new job.

No new artifact.

No magical revelation.

The oval clicked at my building door.

Normal.

Upstairs, Tavi was asleep.

Cala was still awake at the table.

She looked up.

"Good?"

I put my crutches against the wall.

"Marek got slapped."

"Good."

"You don't know why."

"Still good."

Strong audience instinct.

I went upstairs.

Lyssa was awake too.

"How was it?"

I sat on the edge of the bed.

Thought about Hara's fake rain.

The hidden wall.

Marek's early entrance.

Nessa's basket.

Rinna's aisle seat.

The sound cups opening in the dark.

"Better from the front," I said.

Lyssa smiled.

"That's usually where they put the audience."

"Apparently for a reason."

I took the oval from my bag and hung it on the hook.

It turned toward the doorway.

Normal.

I did not write anything down.

That might have been the strangest part.

# CHAPTER 335

## THE BLUE HARNESS

The first thing I wanted badly enough to become ambitious about cost twenty-six copper.

This was inconvenient.

I had two.

The difference between those numbers was not subtle.

It began with a piece of paper nailed crookedly to a post outside the lower market.

Not a work notice.

I checked.

Growth.

The heading read:

SOUTH QUAY FREIGHT SALE

PUBLIC VIEWING TODAY

SALE TOMORROW AFTER THIRD BELL

WAREHOUSE GOODS / CLAIMED LOTS / TOOLS / FITTINGS / THREE REGISTERED WORKING PIECES

NO CHILDREN PAST BLUE LINE

I stopped at `three registered working pieces.`

Of course I did.

Theatre the night before had apparently cured me of writing things down for almost twelve hours.

A personal record.

I read the rest.

Viewing free.

Entry to sale free.

Registered pieces subject to buyer name and transfer fee.

Transfer fee not listed.

Annoying.

South Quay was farther than I had planned to go that morning.

I had planned to buy nothing, do nothing useful, and possibly sit somewhere that sold tea cheaply enough that I could resent other people's purchases.

Instead I turned toward the river.

Ambition began with terrible scheduling.

The lower streets changed as I went.

Less cloth hanging from windows.

More rope.

More carts with wheels thick enough to survive people who thought roads were suggestions.

The smell changed too.

Fish first.

Then wet wood.

Then pitch.

Then river mud underneath everything.

Carrow's south quay sat lower than most of the market district, broad stone ramps descending toward a line of warehouses and loading yards.

I had been near it before.

Not here.

Not this row.

That mattered.

First-life cities had taught me that docks were all the same right until they killed you differently.

Local rules mattered.

Blue paint marked walking lanes between cart routes.

I stayed inside it.

A bell rang twice from somewhere upriver.

Nobody around me reacted.

Not my bell.

Excellent.

Warehouse Twelve had both doors open.

A painted board beside them repeated the sale notice.

A woman at a narrow table looked at my crutches, then at the blue floor line, then at me.

"Viewing?"

"Yes."

"Working pieces are behind rope. Don't touch anything with a red tag. Yellow tag means damaged or incomplete. White means ordinary lot."

"Registered pieces?"

"Blue cards."

"Can I read them?"

"That's why they're there."

Wonderful institution.

"Transfer fee?"

She pointed to a smaller board I had not seen.

REGISTERED TRANSFER: 1 C PER PIECE

There went another copper I did not have.

Not today.

Tomorrow, hypothetically.

Still no.

"Thank you."

"Stay out of cart lane."

"Planning to."

"Everyone is."

Fair.

Inside, the warehouse was colder than the street.

Stone floor.

High rafters.

Light through dusty upper windows.

Rows of sale lots separated by chalk and rope.

Most were exactly what the notice promised.

Tools.

Fittings.

Crates of nails.

Two broken pulley assemblies.

A stack of shutters.

A copper wash basin with a dent large enough to become personality.

Bundles of old sailcloth.

A carpenter's bench.

Three narrow carts.

Nothing magical about most of it.

Good.

Meaningful artifacts were supposed to be rare.

If every warehouse clearance contained six hidden miracles, nobody would clear warehouses.

They would hire more Mara.

The first blue card stood beside a red-painted iron box.

REGISTERED WORKING PIECE 1

HEAT-SAFE STORAGE CHEST

VISIBLE FUNCTION VERIFIED AT INTAKE

INTERIOR COOLING / PASSIVE WHEN LATCHED

HINGE DAMAGE / RIGHT REAR

NO TEMPERATURE CERTIFICATION

OPENING 8 C

Useful.

Obvious.

Eight copper.

I moved on.

The second was a pair of brass clamps mounted on a board.

REGISTERED WORKING PIECE 2

TWIN LOAD CLAMPS

MATCHED PAIR

HOLD UNDER DEMONSTRATED STATIC PULL

RELEASE UNEVEN

NO RATED LOAD

OPENING 11 C

Also useful.

Also expensive.

Also not mine.

Then I saw the blue harness.

It was not blue.

That was the first problem.

Mostly brown leather.

Wide shoulder straps.

A waist belt.

Two narrow metal rails running from shoulder to hip.

Four dark blue thread bundles stitched through the leather in parallel lines.

At the center of the back sat a palm-sized silver housing with a cloudy blue stone under a protective cage.

The whole thing hung from a wooden frame behind rope.

It looked like work equipment.

Old work equipment.

Good work equipment.

The blue card read:

REGISTERED WORKING PIECE 3

ROAD CARRIER'S LOAD HARNESS

MARK: VENN ARLO / SOUTH WORKS

REGISTERED 47 YEARS PRIOR

VISIBLE FUNCTION VERIFIED AT INTAKE

REDUCES CARRIED LOAD DURING CONTINUOUS WALKING

EFFECT FALLS WHEN USER STOPS

TESTED WITH 40-LB SACK / WALKING LOAD SUBJECTIVELY REDUCED

NO RUNNING TEST

NO STAIR TEST

LEFT SHOULDER STRAP REPLACED

CORE CLOUDING PRESENT

OPENING 26 C

I read it twice.

Then a third time because apparently poverty improved literacy.

Venn Arlo.

South Works.

Forty-seven years prior.

That was wrong.

Not the card.

My reaction.

I knew that name.

Not famous now, apparently.

Or famous enough that nobody around me was making noise about it.

In my first life, Venn Arlo had been a name attached to military road gear.

Later road gear.

Much later.

Field bridges.

March frames.

Load-transfer harnesses that officers loved because they could make exhausted people carry more things while calling it efficiency.

I had hated them.

Not because they were bad.

Because they were good enough to encourage bad decisions.

The best ones did not simply make weight lighter.

They changed where load lived while the wearer moved.

Feet.

Ground contact.

Stride timing.

Body rhythm.

Some models could make a pack feel almost polite until you stopped.

Then the world remembered physics.

I stared at the harness.

Forty-seven years prior.

How old had Arlo been then?

I did not know.

That was important.

I knew the later name.

Not this object.

Not this period.

Not whether this was even the same Venn Arlo.

Names repeated.

Workshops reused names.

Sons inherited stamps.

People lied.

Registries helped but did not perform miracles.

Still.

Venn Arlo.

South Works.

My memory pulled a wet military road.

Not a battle.

Worse.

Logistics.

Men complaining under packs.

A quartermaster telling me the new Arlo frames had cut mule demand on the ridge route.

Someone else saying they had doubled knee injuries because commanders immediately increased standard loads.

Humanity.

Give us efficiency and we would use it to invent more suffering.

I looked back at the card.

REDUCES CARRIED LOAD DURING CONTINUOUS WALKING.

EFFECT FALLS WHEN USER STOPS.

That alone justified money.

Twenty-six copper opening money?

Maybe.

For freight work, absolutely maybe.

A porter who could carry more per trip had direct earning value.

A courier.

A caravan hand.

A survey crew.

Anyone moving tools over distance.

The market did not need my future knowledge to see that.

Good.

Then I looked at my crutches.

That was the second problem.

Walking for me was not symmetrical.

Right foot.

Crutches.

Left side absent below knee.

The harness might interpret movement through footfalls.

Or ground contacts.

Or hip translation.

Or repeated displacement.

Or something else entirely.

If it required alternating feet, useless.

If it used total ground-contact rhythm, maybe not.

If it transferred load into every contact point, crutches might count.

That could be incredible.

Or terrible.

A device pushing redistributed load through my shoulders and crutch tips could turn lighter carrying into joint damage.

The card said no stair test.

Nothing about crutches.

Of course not.

I leaned closer without touching the rope.

The replaced left shoulder strap was newer leather.

Different stitching.

Functional-looking.

The blue thread bundles crossed beneath it and disappeared into the silver back housing.

One bundle had been restitched around the replacement.

Not cut.

Rerouted.

Interesting.

"You planning to buy it with your eyes?"

I looked right.

A man stood two spaces away.

Forties maybe.

Shorter than me.

Heavy gray coat despite the warehouse not being that cold.

Hands with rope scars.

Not decorative hands.

He held a folded sale sheet.

"Working on it," I said.

He looked at my crutches.

Then the harness.

Then back at me.

Not pity.

Calculation.

"Wouldn't."

"Why?"

"Stops heavy."

"Card says effect falls when user stops."

"Card is polite."

That got my attention.

"You've used one?"

"Not that one."

Good distinction.

"Same family?"

He shrugged.

"Carrier harness. Old dock crew had two. You walk, load goes friendly. You stop wrong, pack remembers what it weighs."

That matched my first-life category memory.

Not proof about this unit.

"Dangerous?"

"Everything is dangerous if you put a dockhand inside it."

Fair.

"What happened to the two?"

"One sold. One cracked a core."

"From stopping?"

"From being dropped off a cart."

Also humanity.

He looked at the blue card.

"Twenty-six is stupid."

I kept my face neutral.

"Too high?"

"Opening? Yes."

Interesting.

"What would you pay?"

"Eighteen if I needed it. Twenty if the core was clear."

"It's cloudy."

"Exactly."

"You bidding?"

He smiled.

"You?"

"I have two copper."

He laughed once.

Not cruelly.

"Then no."

"Strong analysis."

He moved on.

I watched him go.

No name.

No automatic rival.

Just a man who knew carrier gear and thought the opening price was high.

That mattered too.

Future fame did not erase present condition.

Clouded core.

Replaced strap.

Unknown interaction with my body.

No stair test.

No running test.

Twenty-six opening plus one-copper transfer.

At least twenty-seven just to leave with it if nobody bid higher.

I had two.

This was not a bargain I had failed to notice.

It was an expensive object that might be more important than the current market understood for reasons the current market could not reasonably price.

Different thing.

I wanted it anyway.

That surprised me.

Not because it was magical.

I had seen magical equipment.

Not because it was powerful.

It was a carrying harness.

The thing made sacks less awful while moving.

Very heroic.

I wanted it because I could see my life bending around it if it worked with me.

The shoulder bag became easier.

The holdfast became easier to transport.

Longer walks changed.

Field jobs changed.

Travel changed.

Buying awkward things changed.

Maybe stairs became worse.

Maybe stopping became dangerous.

Maybe my crutches made the entire device useless.

That uncertainty made me want information almost as much as the object.

I found the viewing-table woman.

"Can registered pieces be demonstrated before sale?"

"Intake verification only."

"Can bidder request fit test?"

"No."

"Can buyer inspect after purchase before transfer?"

"Visual only until transfer recorded."

"Can I see intake notes?"

She pointed to another table.

"One bit per copied page. Read here free."

Excellent.

I went there.

The intake sheet for Piece Three was two pages.

I read both.

No copying.

The harness had come from a dissolved freight partnership called Marrow & Pell South Carry.

No relation to marker Pell established.

Probably.

Do not invent relatives because names repeat.

It had been stored six years according to claimant paperwork.

Intake verification used one adult worker, forty-pound sack, level warehouse floor, twenty paces out, twenty back.

Worker reported load reduction after fourth pace.

On stopping, full perceived load returned over approximately one breath.

No fall.

No stumble.

No visible core flare.

Housing warmed slightly.

Second pass not performed because intake clerk considered one verified function enough for sale classification.

Reasonable.

Condition notes:

Left strap replaced before storage.

Blue transfer threads continuous through replacement path.

Core cloudy before test and unchanged after.

Silver housing shows two shallow dents.

Waist clasp stiff.

No registry hold.

No bonded-user notation.

No known injury claim attached to lot.

That was it.

No secret.

No free answer about crutches.

No line saying THIS BECOMES LEGENDARY LATER.

Rude paperwork.

I went back to the harness.

Twenty-six.

Plus transfer.

Maybe more in bidding.

The rope-scarred man was gone.

A woman with a freight company's yellow shoulder stripe was now reading the card.

She had a clerk with her.

The clerk wrote something.

There.

Real buyer.

Of course.

A freight company could value this directly.

I had no special right to it because I remembered a future surname.

I also could not outbid a company with two copper and personality.

Probably.

I left the warehouse.

Outside, the quay was louder.

A cart rolled past carrying bundled poles.

Two porters moved a chest between them.

A boy balanced three empty baskets on his head and looked more stable than I felt emotionally.

I sat on a low stone wall inside the blue pedestrian line.

Twenty-six copper.

My first thought was stupid.

I need twenty-six copper.

No.

Mara had already cured me of saving for yesterday.

If I somehow found twenty-six by tomorrow, I would still need transfer money.

And then I would have almost nothing.

And I would be buying a clouded-core artifact without knowing whether it worked with my gait.

Bad plan.

The useful thought was larger.

I wanted enough money that a twenty-six-copper object could become a decision.

Not today.

Not this harness necessarily.

This category of opportunity.

Twenty-six should be painful.

It should not be fictional.

That was different from wanting twenty-six coins in a pile.

Reserve.

Income.

Access.

Information.

The ability to lose an auction and still eat without composing a tragedy about bread.

I took out the narrow scrap.

Then stopped.

Last night I had not written anything down.

That did not mean notes were bad now.

Growth was not a religion.

I wrote:

SOUTH QUAY / PIECE 3

VENN ARLO / SOUTH WORKS / 47 YRS

ROAD CARRIER LOAD HARNESS

OPEN 26 C + 1 C TRANSFER

LOAD REDUCES AFTER ~4 PACES / RETURNS ~1 BREATH AFTER STOP

40 LB / LEVEL / ONE TEST ONLY

CORE CLOUDY / LEFT STRAP REPLACED / HOUSING DENTS / CLASP STIFF

NO STAIR / RUN / CRUTCH TEST

Then:

FIRST-LIFE NAME RECOGNITION: LATER ARLO ROAD / MILITARY LOAD GEAR

SAME MAKER? UNKNOWN

Then I almost wrote:

NEED 26 C

I did not.

Instead:

NEED ENOUGH THAT 26 C CAN BE A REAL NO

I looked at it.

Too philosophical.

I rubbed out `REAL NO`.

Wrote:

TARGET: RESERVE LARGE ENOUGH TO EVALUATE 20-30 C OPPORTUNITY WITHOUT ZEROING

There.

Ugly.

Useful.

A freight bell rang once.

People moved.

I did not know what the bell meant, so I watched before moving.

Also growth.

A line of carts began crossing the broad ramp.

I waited.

Then headed home by a different street because the quay road was now clogged.

The oval clicked normally at the warehouse door when I had left.

It clicked normally at a stone arch near the upper market.

It did nothing interesting.

Good.

My shoulders were tired by the time I reached home.

The irony was aggressive.

Tavi was on the floor building something from the remaining repair-sweep junk.

Not the oval.

Not the holdfast.

The ordinary pieces.

Cala had apparently decided the obviously mundane buckles and iron hook were safe enough under supervision.

He had made a machine that did nothing.

"What is it?" I asked.

"Carrier."

I stared at him.

"For what?"

He put a wooden spoon across two buckles.
