"Spoon."

Of course.

"How much?"

"Five copper."

"Robbery."

"Four."

"Still robbery."

"Three."

Cala called from the kitchen, "Stop negotiating with him."

"He started it."

"He's six."

"Then his margins are incredible."

I went upstairs.

The holdfast sat on the high shelf.

Two copper away from a simple bench observation if I wanted to become broke.

The oval hung quietly.

One copper of repair-sweep garbage had become one useful unknown object and eighteen pieces of Tavi infrastructure.

And somewhere at South Quay, a twenty-six-copper harness waited for people with more money than me.

I did not feel poor.

That was the strange part.

I felt undercapitalized.

Much more dignified problem.

Also more expensive.

Lyssa came upstairs carrying folded cloth.

"You went far."

"South Quay."

"Why?"

"Free viewing."

She stopped.

"Of what?"

"Bad decisions."

"Did you buy one?"

"Couldn't."

She looked at me.

I corrected myself.

"Wouldn't have. Not with what I have."

"Different."

"Yes."

I told her about the harness.

Not every technical detail.

Twenty-six opening.

Old maker name I recognized from later first-life equipment.

Makes carried weight feel lighter while walking.

Weight returns when you stop.

Unknown with crutches.

Clouded core.

"You want it," she said.

"Yes."

"Because it would help carry things?"

"Maybe."

"Maybe is expensive."

"Very."

She put the folded cloth away.

"Are you going tomorrow?"

I thought about it.

I could not buy.

But the sale itself had information.

What did the market actually pay?

Did twenty-six get no bids?

Did a freight company take it at thirty?

Did two specialists fight it to forty?

Future knowledge was useless if I never calibrated it against current prices.

"Probably," I said.

"To watch?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because you only have two copper."

Hostile household.

I looked at the narrow scrap again.

Twenty-six copper.

One-copper transfer.

Clouded core.

Unknown fit.

Potentially enormous practical value to me.

Potentially ordinary practical value to someone else.

Potentially connected to a maker whose later work I knew.

Not enough to buy.

Enough to care.

That was new.

For most of my second life, money had been about avoiding bad outcomes.

Food.

Debt.

Transport.

Not being trapped.

Then it became choice.

Portrait or no portrait.

Information or reserve.

Now, for the first time, money had become reach.

There were things in this world I wanted to be able to say yes to.

Not because I needed them to survive.

Because they could change what surviving looked like afterward.

Twenty-six copper was absurd.

I smiled anyway.

Good.

Now I had something to aim above.

# CHAPTER 336

## THE BID LINE

I arrived at Warehouse Twelve with two copper and the emotional confidence of a man attending an auction he could not influence.

Strong position.

Nobody could accuse me of overbidding.

The blue pedestrian lanes were busier than yesterday.

Carts queued outside the freight doors.

Men and women with warehouse tags stood in clusters pretending not to inspect each other.

The public entrance had a board now.

SALE ORDER

ORDINARY LOTS FIRST

REGISTERED WORKING PIECES AFTER THIRD BELL

TRANSFER FEE 1 C PER REGISTERED PIECE

NO CREDIT WITHOUT PRIOR ACCOUNT

That last line destroyed several fantasies I had not been having.

I took my place near the back.

Not hidden.

Just poor.

The oval clicked when I crossed the warehouse threshold.

Normal.

Ignored.

Yesterday's display tables had been moved.

The ordinary lots now sat in rows with chalk numbers.

Crates.

Hooks.

Used fittings.

One dented handcart.

Three bundles of rope that looked better from ten feet away than from five.

The registered pieces stayed against the east wall behind a yellow line.

Heat-safe chest.

Twin load clamps.

Blue harness.

The harness looked exactly as expensive as yesterday.

Rude object.

A clerk stood beside it with the blue card.

Another clerk held a ledger.

The freight-company woman from yesterday was there.

Yellow shoulder stripe.

Today she had two other people with her.

One wore the same stripe.

The other did not.

No company name visible.

The gray-coated dock man was also there.

He saw me.

Nodded.

I nodded back.

We had reached the deepest level of male friendship.

Mutual artifact skepticism.

Ordinary lots took longer than expected.

Everything did.

The auctioneer was a square man with a square voice.

He stood on a low platform and sold thirty pounds of bent brackets with more authority than some kings had shown over borders.

"Five bits."

Silence.

"Four."

Hand raised.

"Four bits. Any five?"

No.

"Sold."

Efficient.

Then a box of mismatched hinge plates went for one copper and three bits.

No conversion established.

Excellent.

I learned nothing useful from the arithmetic except that people paid real money for boring things when they needed them.

Which was probably the most useful lesson available.

A cooper bought two iron bands.

A woman with green sleeves bought the dented handcart after arguing with the auctioneer about one wheel.

The auctioneer did not care about the wheel.

The woman cared enough for both of them.

By third bell the ordinary lots were mostly gone.

People shifted.

Different posture.

Different attention.

The registered pieces changed the room.

Not dramatically.

Just enough.

More ledgers came out.

Fewer casual bidders remained near the front.

The auctioneer looked at the first blue card.

"Registered working piece one. Heat-safe storage chest. Verified passive interior cooling when latched. Right rear hinge damaged. No temperature certification. Opening eight copper. Transfer one additional copper."

Eight.

Silence.

Then a hand.

"Eight."

Another.

"Nine."

The first bidder waited.

"Ten."

A woman near the wall said, "Eleven."

No drama.

No gasps.

People were buying equipment.

The chest sold for twelve copper.

Plus transfer.

Thirteen out the door.

I wrote nothing.

I remembered.

Second piece.

Twin brass load clamps.

Opening eleven.

Uneven release.

No rated load.

That one stalled at twelve.

The auctioneer pushed.

"Verified static hold. Matched pair. Registered transfer. Twelve once."

A man in a blue coat raised two fingers.

"Thirteen."

Nobody answered.

Sold thirteen.

Plus transfer.

Fourteen total.

Interesting.

The harness was next.

I knew because the room reorganized again.

The yellow-stripe freight group stepped closer.

Gray coat did not.

He stayed beside me.

"There," he said.

"You bidding?"

"No."

"Thought you said maybe eighteen."

"I said if I needed it."

"You don't."

"I need knees more."

Fair.

The clerk lifted the blue card.

The auctioneer read slowly.

Not because the card was difficult.

Because price liked ceremony.

"Registered working piece three. Road carrier's load harness. Venn Arlo, South Works. Registered forty-seven years. Verified intake function: reduced carried load during continuous walking. Effect falls on stop. Intake test forty-pound sack, level floor, twenty paces out and return. Reduction reported after fourth pace. Full perceived load returned over approximately one breath after stop."

People listened.

"No running test. No stair test. No crutch test. Left shoulder strap replaced before storage. Transfer threads continuous through replacement. Core cloudy before and after intake test. Silver housing two shallow dents. Waist clasp stiff. No registry hold. No known injury claim attached. Opening twenty-six copper. Transfer one copper."

There it was.

Twenty-six.

Silence.

Longer than for the other pieces.

Not because nobody wanted it.

Because twenty-six was a different kind of decision.

The yellow-stripe woman raised her hand.

"Twenty-six."

Immediate.

The auctioneer nodded.

"Twenty-six."

No one else moved.

He waited.

"Twenty-seven?"

A man near the front said, "Twenty-seven."

I had not noticed him yesterday.

Dark vest.

Shaved head.

Ink-stained fingers.

Not dock hands.

Not obvious freight either.

Yellow stripe did not look at him.

"Twenty-eight," she said.

Dark vest said, "Twenty-nine."

Gray coat beside me made a small sound through his nose.

"Still high?" I asked.

"Higher."

Good analysis.

Yellow stripe said, "Thirty."

Now people started watching the bidders instead of the harness.

Human nature.

The auctioneer said, "Thirty copper. Thirty-one?"

Dark vest paused.

Then raised one finger.

"Thirty-one."

The freight woman finally looked at him.

Not angry.

Assessing.

One of her companions leaned close.

Whispered.

She shook her head once.

"Thirty-two."

There.

This was already above the twenty-six opening by enough to matter.

Not absurdly.

Not war-chest money.

But clear demand.

Dark vest looked toward the harness.

Not at the card.

At the cloudy core.

Then at the replaced shoulder strap.

Then at the silver housing dents.

He said, "Thirty-three."

The freight woman said, "Thirty-five."

The room went quieter.

Not shocked.

Interested.

Gray coat said, "Company wants it."

"Apparently."

"Or wants him not to."

That was more interesting.

I looked at dark vest.

No insignia.

No clerk beside him.

But he had a folded document in one hand.

Registry copy maybe.

Inspection note.

Provenance paper.

Something.

He did not bid immediately.

The auctioneer waited.

"Thirty-five copper. Thirty-six?"

Dark vest unfolded the paper.

Read one line.

Then said, "Thirty-six."

Yellow stripe's second companion spoke quietly.

I caught only one word.

"Repair."

The woman asked the auctioneer, "Can registered condition note be reread?"

The auctioneer looked at the clerk.

The clerk said, "Yes."

No one complained.

Useful market.

The clerk read the condition lines again.

Cloudy core.

Two shallow housing dents.

Replaced left shoulder strap.

Stiff waist clasp.

No running, stair, or crutch test.

Then added something I had not heard yesterday.

"Claimant paperwork includes one prior service notation, nineteen years ago. Housing alignment checked after cart fall. No part replacement recorded."

I stared.

Gray coat said, "There it is."

"What?"

"Dents got history."

That mattered.

Not because cart fall meant broken.

Because the dents were no longer generic wear.

They had provenance.

A known event.

Nineteen years ago.

No part replacement recorded.

Could be fine.

Could matter.

The yellow-stripe woman asked, "Any later alignment notation?"

Clerk checked.

"None attached."

Dark vest asked, "Any later bonded inspection?"

"None attached."

The auctioneer said, "Condition as stated. No additional certification. Thirty-six copper standing."

The freight woman did not bid.

Interesting.

The auctioneer waited.

"Thirty-six once."

No movement.

"Thirty-six twice."

Yellow stripe's companion whispered again.

She shook her head.

"Sold. Thirty-six copper. Registered transfer one copper."

Thirty-seven total.

Dark vest had it.

Not mine.

Never plausibly mine.

Still hurt.

Annoying.

The buyer stepped forward.

The clerk took his name too quietly for me to hear.

Good.

No automatic identity gift.

He paid a deposit at the desk.

Not full visible coin.

Prior account maybe.

Or registered bidder terms.

The board had said no credit without prior account.

So he had one.

Another layer.

Artifact access was not just cash.

It was accounts.

Registry.

Transport.

Inspection.

Repair tolerance.

People who could bid thirty-six without carrying thirty-six loose copper in a pocket like a lunatic.

I hated civilization again.

Gray coat said, "Company stopped at thirty-five."

"Because of the cart-fall notation?"

"Maybe."

"You think thirty-six is bad?"

He shrugged.

"Depends what he knows."

There it was.

Perfect answer.

"You'd still stop at twenty?"

"For me? Yes."

"Even after thirty-six sale?"

"Sale price tells me what somebody paid. Not what I should pay."

I looked at him.

"That's annoyingly sensible."

"I work docks."

Apparently that explained philosophy.

The auction moved on to ordinary closing announcements.

People dispersed.

The yellow-stripe group stayed near the clerk.

Not arguing.

Asking something about another lot list.

So losing the harness had not ended their day.

Also useful.

The dark-vest buyer stood near the harness while a warehouse worker adjusted the waist clasp.

He did not put it on.

Good.

No spontaneous demonstration.

No destiny.

I stayed back.

Watching.

The buyer pointed at the replaced strap.

Asked something.

The clerk answered.

He pointed at the silver housing.

More talking.

Then a warehouse porter brought a rigid transport frame.

The harness was tied into it before removal.

That caught me.

Not because transport frames were exotic.

Because yesterday I had been thinking about wearing the thing home.

Of course a thirty-six-copper registered working piece left in a protected frame.

Money changed logistics before function.

I thought of carrying the holdfast against my back in a basket with scrap slats.

Different scale.

Same problem.

Access required infrastructure.

I walked outside.

South Quay smelled like salt, oil, wet rope, and other people's capital.

I sat on a low stone edge beside the pedestrian lane.

Not long.

Enough to rest shoulders.

Two copper in my purse.

Thirty-six sale price in my head.

Thirty-seven with transfer.

The target line I had written yesterday suddenly looked small.

Twenty to thirty copper opportunity.

The harness had opened at twenty-six and left at thirty-six.

That did not mean all such opportunities would.

It meant reserve had to include bidding uncertainty too.

Not just asking price.

If I had somehow arrived with twenty-seven exactly, I would have learned nothing except how fast twenty-seven stopped mattering.

I took out the narrow scrap.

I had said yesterday I would not write `NEED 26 C.`

Good.

Still correct.

Under the reserve line I added:

OPEN 26 / SOLD 36 / +1 TRANSFER

Then:

PRICE CAN MOVE ~10 C BEFORE DECISION ENDS

I stared at that.

Too specific.

One auction.

One object.

I crossed out the ten.

Wrote:

OPENING PRICE != BUYING PRICE

Then:

ACCESS = CASH + ACCOUNT + INSPECTION + TRANSPORT + REPAIR ROOM

That was uglier.

Also better.

I underlined REPAIR ROOM once.

The cart-fall notation had not destroyed the harness.

It had changed who stayed in.

The freight company stopped at thirty-five.

The private buyer went thirty-six.

Maybe he had cheaper repair access.

Maybe he valued the maker.

Maybe he knew Venn Arlo.

Maybe he was simply wrong.

Markets were allowed to contain confident idiots too.

Rational did not mean omniscient.

Useful distinction.

I did not write that.

I was becoming selective.

Dangerous.

On the walk home, the oval clicked at South Quay gate.

Normal.

At the building door.

Normal.

At the upstairs doorway.

Normal.

The holdfast sat on its shelf.

Two copper between me and Mara's simple bench observation.

Still not doing it.

That surprised me less now.

The harness sale had made the reason clearer.

Zero was not just no money.

Zero was no ability to react when the next useful thing appeared.

Even if I could not afford the next useful thing yet.

Reserve was practice for larger reserve.

That sounded unbearably mature.

I hated it.

Lyssa came in while I was looking at the scrap.

"How much?"

"Thirty-six."

She stopped.

"Copper?"

"Yes. Plus one transfer."

"You were not close."

"Thank you."

"You knew that."

"I preferred my version."

She sat across from me.

"Who bought it?"

"Man in dark vest. Didn't hear name. Freight company stopped at thirty-five."

"Why?"

"Condition note. Old cart fall, maybe. Or price. Or repair."

"You don't know."

"Correct."

She nodded.

Then looked at the scrap.

"What did you learn?"

I slid it over.

She read:

OPENING PRICE != BUYING PRICE

ACCESS = CASH + ACCOUNT + INSPECTION + TRANSPORT + REPAIR ROOM

"That's more expensive than yesterday."

"Everything becomes more expensive when understood."

"Not everything."

"Name one thing."

She looked toward the kitchen.

"Tavi."

From downstairs Tavi shouted, "I HEARD THAT."

Lyssa smiled.

"Cheaper every day."

Good household economics.

I took the scrap back.

Thirty-six copper.

I still wanted the harness.

That was the inconvenient part.

Not that harness specifically.

Maybe that one.

Yes, that one.

But more than that, I wanted to live in a financial shape where seeing something like it did not immediately place me outside the room.

Not rich.

Ready.

Different ambition.

I looked at the two copper.

Then at the holdfast.

Then at the oval hanging quietly.

One had cost two.

One had cost one.

Both had taught me more than their prices.

The harness had cost me nothing.

It had still changed what money meant.

Annoying object.

I hoped the dark-vest man enjoyed it.

Not too much.

# CHAPTER 337

## THE RED PATH

The next morning, Cala sent me to buy onions.

This was probably good for me.

Thirty-six-copper artifacts were dangerous to a man's sense of scale.

Onions corrected that.

"Six," Cala said.

"Large?"

"Normal."

"Define normal."

"The ones that look like onions."

Strong procurement standard.

Tavi was at the table building another impossible machine from the remaining repair-sweep junk.

Today the wooden spoon had acquired two metal loops and a bent strip of brass.

"Carrier," he said.

"Still?"

"Faster one."

"Does it move?"

"No."

"Excellent safety profile."

Cala handed me the market cloth.

"Onions."

"I understand."

"You came home yesterday talking about accounts and repair room."

"I was asked what I learned."

"Today learn onions."

I left.

The oval was in my shoulder bag.

Not because I expected anything.

Because it had become something I carried.

That was different from something I tested.

Front door.

Click.

Normal.

Street.

Morning traffic.

Normal.

The temporary black conduit still ran along the upper front-hall wall, but I no longer looked at it every time I passed.

That was also progress.

Carrow was damp from night rain.

Not wet enough for puddles everywhere.

Wet enough that cart wheels had darkened the grooves between stones and every wooden step looked slightly more suspicious than yesterday.

I took the lower market route.

Not because onions were better there.

Because the upper lane had three stairs in a row outside a baker and I was feeling lazy.

Disability was sometimes philosophy.

Mostly it was route selection.

Halfway down Tanner's Cut, I saw the red stakes.

Four of them.

Thin wood.

Fresh paint.

One at each corner of a section of street where the paving changed from gray stone to older brown blocks.

A red cord ran between them at knee height.

A municipal board leaned against the wall.

CHANNEL WORK

PEDESTRIANS USE EAST WALK

NO CARTS AFTER SECOND BELL

Fine.

Street work.

I crossed to the east side.

So did everyone else.

A woman with two baskets complained.

A man carrying folded hides complained louder.

Neither complaint changed the road.

Useful government.

Then the ground moved.

Not much.

Enough.

The brown paving inside the red cord rose perhaps half an inch in a line from one stake to the next.

A soft grinding sound came from underneath.

People stopped.

I stopped too.

A municipal worker inside the cord shouted, "Keep walking. East side clear."

People resumed.

I did not.

Not because danger.

Because the raised line was moving.

Slowly.

A ridge under stone.

Then another ridge formed behind it.

The first sank.

The second rose.

Something traveled beneath the street.

I knew the category.

Not this implementation.

Buried service crawler.

Ground mole.

Channel worm.

Different regions had different names and different levels of honesty about whether the things were alive.

This one was probably not alive.

Probably.

The municipal worker saw me watching.

"East side."

"I'm on east side."

"Then keep moving."

Fair.

I kept moving.

The ridge followed the cord line under the brown blocks.

At the next stake, a metal cap in the street lifted by itself.

Not dramatically.

It rose on three little arms.

The buried thing stopped beneath it.

A second worker crouched and fitted a black fork into the opening.

The fork vibrated.

The first worker looked at a strip of pale paper clipped to a board.

"Still red?"

"Red."

"How red?"

"Don't be clever."

"I'm asking."

"Third line."

"That's not good."

There.

A problem.

Not mine.

I walked on.

Then heard the first worker say, "West branch or old drain?"

The second said, "Map says west branch."

"Street says old drain."

I stopped again.

Not because I was going to solve it.

Because I had heard that sentence in twelve different forms across two lives.

Map says one thing.

Ground says another.

The ground usually won.

A third person approached from the far end of the work zone.

Woman.

Green coat.

Mud to the knees.

Short iron staff with a brass wheel near the grip.

She ducked under the red cord.

"Where?"

First worker pointed at the lifted cap.

"Third line. Map puts branch west. Crawler keeps pulling south."

"You let it pull?"

"Two lengths."

"Why?"

"Because map puts branch west."

The woman looked at him.

He looked away.

Professional communication.

She crouched by the cap.

Turned the brass wheel on her staff.

The iron shaft hummed.

Not mana I could feel directly.

External tool response.

She pressed the staff against the street.

The hum deepened.

Then shifted pitch.

She moved three feet south.

Again.

Another three.

Again.

"Old drain," she said.

The first worker swore.

"Registered?"

"Not on this sheet."

"Then what is it doing under our channel?"

"Existing."

Excellent answer.

The woman stood.

"Stop crawler. Open south cap. Hand trace before you move anything else."

"That adds half a day."

"Less than breaking a drain."

"Probably."

"Definitely."

I liked her too.

Carrow contained too many competent women. It was terrible for my ego.

I resumed my onion mission.

Then the oval clicked.

I had not crossed anything.

I stopped.

The sound was quiet inside the bag.

Not sharp.

One click.

I did not reach for it.

The municipal woman called, "You. Crutches."

I looked around.

There were no other crutches.

Unfortunate.

"Yes?"

"Don't stop there."

I looked down.

The east walk beneath me was ordinary gray paving.

"Why?"

"Crawler's turning under the walk."

I moved.

Immediately.

Not because oval.

Because professional with vibrating ground stick.

Three steps forward.

The paving behind me gave a low scrape.

One stone shifted perhaps a finger-width.

Nothing exploded.

Nobody screamed.

The worker moved the pedestrian cord farther east.

"Use wall side," he called.

People adjusted.

I adjusted.

The oval had clicked before the warning.

Interesting.

Still not proof.

The crawler had been turning under the walk.

Maybe the object responded to changing retreat geometry.

Maybe it responded to the worker crossing some invisible service boundary.

Maybe it clicked because a cart two streets away offended it spiritually.

I bought onions.

Six.

Normal-looking.

Cala would be proud.

On the way back, Tanner's Cut was more disrupted.

The south cap was open.

A square section of paving had been lifted.

Inside was a narrow brick-lined channel running diagonally beneath the street.

Old.

Wet.

Very real.

The buried crawler was now visible too.

Only part of it.

A segmented brass-and-black body about as thick as my thigh sat inside the municipal service groove.

Not alive.

Probably definitely now.

Each segment had little folding feet pressed against the channel walls.

A cable ran from its rear to a spool cart.

At the front, three flat metal whiskers spread against the brick.

The green-coated woman was kneeling beside it.

The first worker held the black fork.

"Hand trace says drain cuts across channel for eleven feet," he said.

"Depth?"

"Changes."

"Of course it does."

"Old work."

"Old work isn't an excuse for being old."

"I think it is exactly the excuse."

She looked at him.

He smiled.

Ah.

They knew each other.

The world continued without me.

Good.

I slowed because the pedestrian lane narrowed around the opened section.

The woman saw me.

"You came back."

I lifted the market cloth.

"Onions."

She nodded solemnly.

"Critical route."

"Apparently."

"Wall side."

I took wall side.

Then she said, "Wait."

I waited.

She looked at my crutches.

Then at the gap between the wall and the moved cord.

"Too narrow?"

"I can fit."

"Can you turn if someone comes opposite?"

I looked.

No.

"Not well."

She pointed at the cord.

"Drop that stake."

The first worker pulled one red stake and lowered the cord to the ground, widening the path by about two feet.

"Go."

I went.

Simple.

No speech.

No heroic accessibility lesson.

Someone saw geometry and changed it.

I appreciated civilization again.

For nearly six minutes.

Then a cart tried to enter Tanner's Cut despite the board saying no carts after second bell.

The driver argued with the worker.

Civilization reverted.

At home, Cala inspected the onions.

Actually inspected them.

"Good."

"I have achieved produce."

"You took a long time."

"Street was being eaten."

Tavi looked up.

"By what?"

"Government."

Cala threw a cloth at me.

I caught it badly.

Lyssa was near the window working on a sleeve.

"What actually happened?"

I told them.

Buried service crawler.

Old drain not on current sheet.

Hand trace.

Half-day delay.

Pedestrian route moved.

Tavi asked, "Did it have teeth?"

"No."

"Then not eating."

"Strong objection."

Cala started cutting onions.

I sat.

The oval clicked when Tavi crossed the kitchen doorway.

Normal.

I took it out later.

Not immediately.

After lunch.

After the onions had become part of something better than onions.

After the house was quiet enough that I was not turning one click into a family event.

I hung the oval from its cord near the upstairs doorway.

It pointed back toward the threshold.

Normal.

I crossed.

Click.

Normal.

Returned.

Click.

Normal.

I did not recreate Tanner's Cut with chairs.

This was growth.

I did take out the narrow scrap.

For a while I wrote nothing.

The useful observation was simple.

OVAL CLICKED BEFORE MUNICIPAL WARNING / CRAWLER TURNING UNDER EAST WALK

I stared.

Too dramatic.

It implied connection.

I crossed out BEFORE MUNICIPAL WARNING.

Wrote:

TANNER'S CUT: NO-CROSSING CLICK WHILE SERVICE ROUTE / PEDESTRIAN ROUTE CHANGING

Better.

Then:

NO EVENT AFTER MOVING EXCEPT MINOR PAVING SHIFT

Also true.

Then I stopped.

The more interesting thing had not been the oval.

It had been the old drain.

A current map said west branch.

The ground said diagonal drain.

The specialist believed the ground.

That should not have been profound.

It wasn't.

It was just useful.

I thought about the marker field with Antonius.

Filed line versus physical posts.

Mara's housing read.

Auction condition notes.

Hessa separating target distance from effect magnitude.

Different domains.

Same irritating fact.

Descriptions were not objects.

Maps were not streets.

Prices were not value.

A clean result was not a whole ability.

I almost wrote that down.

Then did not.

Too broad.

I was becoming allergic to wisdom.

Healthy.

Someone knocked downstairs.

Front door.

Normal door now.

I heard Cala answer.

A man's voice.

Not urgent.

Then footsteps.

Cala called up.

"Greg."

"What?"

"For you."

That was suspicious.

I went downstairs.

A boy perhaps fifteen stood inside the front hall holding a folded paper.

Not someone I knew.

Courier strap across his chest.

He held out the paper.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"From East Kiln gate."

I took it.

The paper was folded once and sealed with a square smear of black wax.

No formal crest.

Just a thumb mark pressed through the wax.

The boy said, "No reply paid."

"Good. I wasn't prepared."

He did not laugh.

Professional youth.

Then left.

The oval clicked when the front door closed.

Normal.

I opened the paper.

Four lines.

GREG,

BLACK CHIT STILL GOOD.

PUBLIC COUNTER AFTER FIRST BELL TOMORROW.

JESSA.

I read it twice.

Not because complicated.

Because I had almost forgotten the black yard chit.

Not forgotten forgotten.

Background forgotten.

The best kind of continuity.

Kiln Street.

Counterload frame.

Emergency message.

Jessa.

East gate public counter.

The square black chit was still in my bag's inner fold.

I checked.

There.

No job promised.

No tools.

No training.

No store access.

Just the narrow thing it had always been.

Access.

I looked at the note from the auction.

ACCESS = CASH + ACCOUNT + INSPECTION + TRANSPORT + REPAIR ROOM

Apparently I had missed one.

People remembering you.

I did not add it to the equation.

That would have been unbearable.

I folded Jessa's note and put it beside the chit.

Tomorrow after first bell.

Fine.

I had onions today.

A yard gate tomorrow.

Thirty-six-copper ambitions somewhere above both.

That felt more like a life than a plan.

# CHAPTER 338

## THE PUBLIC COUNTER

I arrived after first bell with the black chit in my bag and no idea what it was for.

This was becoming one of my more successful strategies.

East Kiln smelled like wet stone, hot metal, lime dust, and breakfast eaten too close to work.

Deliveries had already started.

That mattered because the note had said after first bell.

Before first bell, apparently, I was a hazard.

After first bell, I was paperwork.

Progress.

The East gate was not where I remembered it.

Not moved.

Just busier.

Two carts waited outside.

One empty.

One carrying squared stone under canvas.

A woman with a slate checked markings against the cart sides while a boy rolled a wheel under the rear axle and called numbers.

No one looked dramatic.

Good yard.

I stayed outside the gate line.

The black chit was in my hand.

Not raised.

I had enough dignity left not to wave it around like a child who had found permission.

A gate worker glanced at me.

Then at the chit.

"Public counter?"

"Apparently."

He pointed through the gate.

"Yellow line. Follow it. Don't cross white stone."

That was it.

No inspection.

No speech.

The chit did not glow.

Disappointing artifact design.

I followed the yellow line.

The yard was larger from inside.

That was stupid.

Obviously it was the same size.

But streets flattened places into walls and gates.

Inside, the cooperative had depth.

Covered sheds.

Stone racks.

Hoists.

Low rail carts.

Three load frames in different stages of use.

One of them had the same broad geometry as the frame that had failed weeks ago.

Not same machine.

Same family.

Counterload mast.

Low bed.

Guide arms.

Colored lever positions.

I did not stare long enough to get yelled at.

Yellow line.

Public counter.

The counter was literally a counter.

Wood.

Chest high.

Under a narrow awning along the inner wall.

Three windows.

RETURNS

CLAIMS

SURPLUS / RETIRED

Ah.

That was interesting.

Jessa stood beside the third window holding a mug.

She saw me.

"You came."

"You sent a courier."

"Some people still ignore those."

"I respect paid logistics."

"Courier wasn't paid for reply."

"He made that clear."

Jessa nodded.

"Good kid."

No further biography.

Excellent.

I held up the chit.

"Still good, apparently."

"For the public counter."

"I was worried it might secretly make me a mason."

"It doesn't."

"Good."

"You'd be terrible."

Less good.

She took a drink.

"Davin said you kept looking at the frame after the lift."

"I keep looking at most things."

"I noticed."

"Concerning."

She pointed at the third window.

"Surplus turns over today. Thought you might like seeing what happens to old yard gear when we stop trusting it."

That was better than a job.

Possibly worse for my finances.

"Do I need the chit to buy?"

"No."

Interesting.

"Then what does the chit do?"

"Gets you from gate to counter without someone walking you."

I looked at the square black piece in my hand.

That was it.

Access.

Literal access.

Not discount.

Not authority.

Not secret inventory.

A permitted route.

I liked it more immediately.

"Can anyone use the counter?"

"Public counter."

"Strong clue."

"Gate's working yard. Public doesn't wander through it. Chit means gate knows you're allowed to walk the marked line alone."

There.

Small privilege.

Real privilege.

Narrow enough not to become destiny.

Perfect.

The SURPLUS / RETIRED window had a slate board beside it.

Six lines.

Not six items.

Six categories.

STONE HARDWARE

FRAME FITTINGS

WORN HAND TOOLS

NONRATED LOAD PARTS

REGISTERED COMPONENTS AS MARKED

MIXED / BY WEIGHT

Prices were written beside categories, not individual magical objects.

Most were bits.

A few one-copper lots.

Two lines said ASK.

I hated ASK.

ASK was how money acquired personality.

Behind the counter sat shallow bins.

Iron collars.

Pins.

Bent hooks.

Wood blocks.

Leather straps.

Metal plates.

Enough ordinary industrial junk to make Tavi ascend spiritually.

A man behind the window looked at Jessa.

Then me.

"Friend?"

"Witness from south-lift mess."

"The runner?"

"Message carrier."

Good correction.

The man nodded once.

"Chit?"

I showed it.

He did not take it.

"Fine. Counter only."

"I have been warned about my masonry career."

He stared.

Jessa drank from her mug.

Traitor.

The man slid a flat board onto the counter.

Seven objects had been tied to it with cord.

Each had a small paper card.

"Today's marked components," he said.

I looked at Jessa.

"You arranged this?"

"No. Thursday arranged it."

Even better.

The world had systems.

The first was a brass guide roller.

No core.

No mark beyond yard stamp.

Retired for groove wear.

Two bits.

Second was a red-handled load pin.

Plain steel.

Retired because shaft measured narrow.

One bit.

Third was a thumb-sized black stone set inside a split iron collar.

Blue card.

There.

Registered component.

I leaned closer.

Did not touch.

The card read:

COUNTERLOAD REST STONE / SMALL

YARD REGISTER K-14-6

FUNCTION AT LAST CHECK: HOLDS FRAME BIAS WHILE MAIN LOAD IS STATIC

REMOVED FROM WEST BED TWO

REASON: RELEASE DELAY OUTSIDE YARD LIMIT

NO RATED USE

SALE AS RETIRED COMPONENT

PRICE 1 C

I read it twice.

One copper.

There were cheap magical things.

Of course there were.

Not powerful magical things.

Not trustworthy ones.

Not complete systems.

Components.

Used.

Retired.

Functionally narrow.

Potentially dangerous if misunderstood.

The market had a floor and a ceiling and a basement full of bad decisions.

I loved it.

Jessa watched my face.

"That's why I sent the note."

"You want me dead?"

"No. You asked Davin what happened to broken frame parts."

I had.

Apparently.

I vaguely remembered asking while everyone was still sweaty and annoyed.

The problem with saying many things was that other people occasionally remembered them.

"This came from that frame?"

"No."

Immediate.

Good.

"West bed two. Different assembly."

"Same family?"

"Maybe. Ask counter."

The man behind the window said, "Counterload part. Don't know if same pattern."

I nodded.

No free expertise from proximity.

I read the card again.

Holds frame bias while main load static.

Release delay outside yard limit.

That was not a minor defect.

Depending on system, delayed release could mean the frame kept expecting a load after the load changed.

Or held tension too long.

Or made the next movement wrong.

Or something else entirely.

I knew enough to be afraid in several directions.

Excellent.

"What does sale as retired component permit?"

The counter man answered.

"Ownership. Not use approval."

"Can it legally be mounted?"

"On what?"

"Anything."

"Then no answer."

Good.

"Can it be tested?"

"By someone who wants to test it."

"Here?"

"No."

"Do you have condition notes beyond card?"

"Public copy is card. Yard sheet isn't sale document."

There.

Information boundary.

I could buy the object for one copper and still not buy the cooperative's internal history.

"Can I inspect it without touching?"

"You're doing that."

I liked him more now.

The split iron collar was ordinary enough.

The stone inside was not.

Black at first.

Then dark red when light caught one edge.

Not glowing.

Translucent deeper in.

A shallow cut crossed the exposed face.

Not a maker mark.

Could be wear.

Could be installation witness line.

Could be someone with a knife and poor impulse control.

The collar had two bolt holes.

One slightly oval from wear.

The card said release delay.

I wanted it.

Immediately.

That was suspicious.

One copper was half my liquid money.

But not all.

That distinction mattered now.

I could buy it and still have one copper.

The old version of my finances would have turned this into disaster arithmetic.

The new version was simpler.

Did I have a use?

No.

Could I evaluate it well enough to know hidden value?

No.

Was one copper cheap relative to magical component exposure?

Probably.

Was cheap enough the same as worth buying?

No.

Annoying growth.

I looked at the other blue-card component.

Small rectangular copper plate with two white ceramic studs.

Card:

HEAT SPREAD PLATE

REGISTER K-22-3

FUNCTION: REDUCES LOCAL HOT SPOT UNDER CONTINUOUS CONTACT

REMOVED: EDGE CRACK

NOT FOR PRESSURE SERVICE

PRICE 2 BITS

Obvious.

Useful.

Damaged.

Cheap.

The crack ran from one corner almost to the nearest stud.

I did not want it.

Which was useful information about me.

Third marked piece was stranger.

A narrow iron fork with one tine shorter.

No blue card.

Yellow.

INCOMPLETE GUIDE FORK / MATCHED MATE MISSING / 1 BIT.

Mundane or maybe system-dependent magical geometry.

Not enough information.

The rest were ordinary retired parts.

Jessa finished her drink.

"Well?"

"Dangerous place."

"It's a counter."

"Exactly."

She nodded toward the rest stone.

"You're staring at the one-copper piece."

"It is staring back."

"No, it isn't."

"You don't know."

"I removed it."

That got me.

"You?"

"With two others."

Good correction before I made her magical stone surgeon.

"What was the release delay?"

She looked at the counter man.

He looked back.

"Public card," he said.

Right.

Jessa shrugged.

"Then that's what you get."

I appreciated that too.

Workplace knowledge was not automatically mine because someone liked me enough to send a note.

I asked, "Why sell it at all?"

Counter man said, "Because retired isn't cursed."

Beautiful.

"And because somebody buys metal and stone," Jessa added.

"For repair?"

"Sometimes."

"Study?"

"Sometimes."

"Parts?"

"Sometimes."

"Bad ideas?"

"Frequently."

There it was.

A whole secondary market under the polished artifact market.

Not hidden treasure.

Retired industrial magic.

Known faults.

Incomplete records.

Narrow legal transfer.

Buyers who might want material, comparison pieces, repair stock, or terrible experiments.

One copper suddenly felt less like a price and more like an admission fee to a category.

Which was exactly how I got in trouble.

I stepped back from the board.

"Not buying yet."

Jessa raised an eyebrow.

"Yet?"

"Dangerous word."

"You have until counter closes."

"Today only?"

"Marked board turns weekly. Unsold may roll, may go mixed, may go scrap."

That mattered.

Not fake urgency.

Actual inventory turnover.

I looked again.

One copper.

A retired rest stone with known release delay.

No use approval.

No test access here.

No detailed yard sheet.

If I bought it, I could bring it to Mara eventually.

Which would probably cost more than the component.

Artifacts were very good at generating service economies around themselves.

I hated them.

Jessa said, "Come on."

"Where?"

"Counter side."

"Chit says counter only."

"Yes."

"That sounded like leaving counter."

"It isn't."

She walked ten feet along the awning.

I followed the yellow strip painted in front of the counter windows.

Still public route.

At the far end was a waist-high iron stand bolted to the wall.

On it sat a large cracked block of pale stone with three metal loops embedded through it.

A sign above it read:

FAILED / RETIRED EXAMPLES

DO NOT REMOVE

Oh.

I loved civilization again.

"What's this?"

"People kept asking what failure notes mean."

"So you made a museum?"

"We put broken things by the counter."

"Museum."

"Don't call it that."

I would absolutely call it that.

There were five examples.

A bent load pin cut halfway through to show an internal crack.

A guide roller with a groove worn deep enough to shift alignment.

A ceramic heat plate with a repaired edge that had cracked again through the repair.

A braided red cord with one section glazed dark from heat.

And the pale stone block.

Its face had a clean-looking line across it.

Too clean.

The sign below said:

LOAD STONE / INTERNAL FRACTURE FOUND AFTER RINGING TEST

EXTERIOR LINE WAS POLISH MARK, NOT CRACK

FAILED BELOW SURFACE

I stared.

That was almost rude.

Visible mark not crack.

Hidden crack elsewhere.

The exact opposite of what a nervous buyer would assume.

Jessa tapped the sign, not the stone.

"We show this because people point at scratches and decide they understand condition."

"I know someone who would like you."

"Who?"

"Mara Quill."

"Don't know her."

Good.

Carrow was not secretly twelve people.

I looked at the cut pin.

"Can I ask something?"

"You have been."

"Why show public buyers failure examples if you can just sell scrap?"

Jessa shrugged.

"Fewer arguments."

That was possibly the most realistic institutional answer available.

The counter man called from the window, "And fewer returns."

"Returns on retired parts?"

"People try."

Of course they did.

A broad man in an apron approached the counter carrying a broken clamp.

We moved aside.

He placed it at RETURNS.

Different window.

Different problem.

The clerk there asked for yard mark.

The man gave a number.

They argued about whether one jaw had been filed after issue.

Not loudly.

Professionally.

Jessa waited until they were occupied.

"You helped on the lift because you carried the message exactly."

I looked at her.

"That sounds dangerously like praise."

"Don't get used to it."

"Good."

"You also didn't touch anything."

"Very skilled."

"You'd be surprised."

I would not.

She nodded toward the counter.

"So if you want to look, look. If you want to buy, buy. If you want yard work, this chit does not get you yard work."

"Understood."

"If someone offers you work, they tell you."

"Also understood."

"If you cross white stone, somebody yells."

"Strong system."

"Very old."

That was the whole invitation.

Not mentorship.

Not recruitment.

A worker deciding that a curious person who had behaved usefully once could be trusted to walk to the public-facing edge of the yard without supervision.

Small.

Huge.

I hated how often those were the same thing.

Jessa left me there.

Actually left.

"Shift," she said.

Then ducked under a chain and disappeared toward the load beds.

No tour.

No special demonstration.

I stood at the public counter alone.

That improved the experience.

I spent another half hour looking.

Not only at the rest stone.

That was important.

A woman bought four worn iron collars by weight.

A young man asked whether a yellow-tagged guide fork could be returned if he found its mate did not fit.

No.

A carpenter bought two retired leather straps after checking stitch holes.

A gray-haired man bought the cracked heat plate for two bits and told the clerk he wanted the ceramic studs.

There.

Not function.

Parts value.

The rest stone stayed.

A worker in a blue apron looked at it, read the card, asked if collar iron was included, then left.

Different valuation.

I learned more from that than from staring.

At one point the counter man changed the card on a bin of mixed fittings from one copper by basket to three bits because enough of the better iron had sold.

Dynamic pricing at the smallest scale.

Civilization was relentless.

I had two copper.

The rest stone cost one.

I could afford it.

That was not the same as I should buy it.

I thought about the harness.

Thirty-six copper.

Too far to act.

This was one copper.

Close enough to act.

The problem was almost harder.

When something was impossible, decision-making was cheap.

When something was possible, I had to be an adult.

Unfair.

What did I actually gain from ownership?

A physical registered retired component.

A known function at last check.

A known failure mode broad enough to matter.

A piece I could compare against future counterload parts.

Maybe a thing Mara could inspect later.

Maybe something I could learn from without activating.

What did I risk?

Half my cash reserve.

Storage.

Future appraisal cost.

Temptation to do something stupid because I owned it.

Potentially possessing a component I could not safely use.

One copper was not nothing.

It also was not existential.

That distinction had to mean something eventually or the economy model was fake.

I asked the counter man, "Does registration transfer with the component?"

"Sale slip lists old register number and retired status. Not active equipment registration."

"Fee?"

"Included at counter."

That mattered.

No extra copper surprise.

"Can I get a copy of the public card?"

"Card goes with it."

"Original?"

"Sale card. Not yard sheet."

Right.

I looked at the stone.

Then my purse.

Then stone.

I bought it.

One copper.

No thunder.

No destiny.

The counter man untied it from the display board.

He did not hand it directly to me.

He wrapped the iron collar and stone in coarse packing cloth, then placed it in a shallow wooden box barely larger than my palm.

"Keep card with it."

"I was planning to."

"People say that."

"You have a low opinion of buyers."

"I work returns."

Fair.

He wrote a sale slip.

One copper.

Retired counterload rest stone.

Register K-14-6.

No rated use.

Public card attached.

I paid.

One copper remained.

Not zero.

That felt important.

Also terrifying.

I put the shallow box in my shoulder bag.

It was heavier than the oval but not difficult.

The oval sat in its own wrapped section.

Two artifacts in one bag.

One working enough to be annoying.

One retired enough to be officially untrusted.

Excellent life choices.

At the gate, the worker glanced at the box.

"Slip?"

I showed it.

He looked at the register line.

Then waved me through.

The black chit stayed with me.

I stopped.

"Do you keep this?"

"Chit? No."

"Still good?"

"Until marked otherwise."

"How would I know?"

"Gate tells you."

Ancient technology.

I left East Kiln with one copper, one retired magical component, and a much more dangerous understanding of public infrastructure.

There were places where magical objects did not enter the world as legendary treasure.

They aged out of work.

They cracked.

They missed tolerances.

They got stripped for parts.

They moved through counters for bits and copper because their original use no longer justified trust.

That did not make them secretly valuable.

It made them available.

Different thing.

Possibly more useful.

At home, I put the box on the table.

Cala looked at it.

Then me.

"What did you buy?"

"A retired failure."

"Why?"

"It was one copper."

"That did not answer me."

Lyssa looked over from the window.

"He found cheap magic."

"Retired magic," I said.

"Worse," Cala said.

Tavi appeared from nowhere.

"Can I touch?"

"No."

"Can I see?"

"From there."

He leaned over the table from a medically unnecessary distance.

I unwrapped the box but did not remove the stone.

Read the card aloud.

COUNTERLOAD REST STONE.

Holds frame bias while main load static.

Removed for release delay outside yard limit.

No rated use.

Cala looked at me.

"What does that mean?"

"It means we do not use it."

"Good."

"Yet."

Her expression changed.

"Greg."

"For observation."

"You said yet."

"I regret language."

Lyssa smiled.

Tavi pointed at the stone.

"Does it make things lighter?"

"No idea."

"Does it hold things?"

"In a system, apparently."

"Can it hold spoon carrier?"

"Absolutely not."

His disappointment was profound.

I moved the box upstairs.

Not onto the holdfast shelf.

That shelf already contained enough poor judgment.

Different shelf.

Lower than holdfast but above Tavi reach.

The card stayed under the box lid.

Sale slip folded beneath it.

I wrote only three new lines on the narrow scrap.

EAST KILN PUBLIC COUNTER: RETIRED COMPONENTS / KNOWN FAULTS / NO USE APPROVAL

K-14-6 REST STONE / 1 C / RELEASE DELAY

CASH NOW 1 C

Then stopped.

I almost wrote:

CHEAP MAGIC EXISTS BELOW COMPLETE-ARTIFACT MARKET

Too broad.

Cheap retired components existed.

Different claim.

The distinction mattered.

I crossed nothing out because I had not written the stupid version.

More growth.

The holdfast waited above my clothes.

The oval hung near the doorway.

The rest stone sat in its shallow box.

Three objects.

Three completely different relationships to trust.

The oval was useful but unexplained.

The holdfast was demonstrated but unresolved.

The rest stone was documented and retired for a known problem.

Information did not move in one direction.

Sometimes the cheapest object came with the clearest warning.

That felt unfair.

Also healthy.

Downstairs, Cala called that food was ready.

I went.

The stone stayed on the shelf.

Which was, for the moment, the smartest thing I knew how to do with it.

# CHAPTER 339

## THE SECOND VOICE

The next thing I bought was nothing.

This was financially responsible and emotionally unsatisfying.

The rest stone stayed in its box.

The holdfast stayed on its shelf.

The oval stayed in my bag because apparently I had decided one badly understood object should accompany me through ordinary life.

One copper stayed in my purse.

For two whole mornings.

I was becoming wealthy through inactivity.

Then Rinna sent a child to find me.

Not Tavi.

A theatre child.

This distinction mattered because theatre children arrived with messages and Tavi arrived with objects he had improved into danger.

The girl stood in the front hall holding a folded scrap.

"Rinna says if you're free before second bell, come by."

"Why?"

"She didn't say."

"Excellent management."

The girl shrugged.

"She said bring your crutches."

I looked down.

"I was planning to."

She smiled.

Good.

Somebody appreciated me.

I went upstairs for my shoulder bag.

Lyssa was working near the window.

"Theatre?"

"Apparently."

"Paid?"

"Apparently not enough information."

"Don't volunteer for six hours."

"I never volunteer for six hours."

She looked at me.

I corrected myself.

"I rarely intend to volunteer for six hours."

Better.

At the theatre, the front doors were open.

Not for audience.

Air.

The place smelled like dust, cloth, old wood, lamp oil, and yesterday's rain dragged in on shoes.

House left was empty.

My seat from the performance was just a chair again.

I liked that.

Things should be allowed to stop being important.

Rinna was standing near the stage with Nessa and Jori.

A long wooden case sat open on the floor.

Inside were four narrow brass tubes, each perhaps the length of my forearm.

Not weapons.

Probably.

One had a flared mouth.

One had a square collar.

Two were plain except for tiny silver bands near the ends.

Marek was onstage saying, "I maintain it improved the scene."

Hara said, "You entered from the wrong door."

"With conviction."

"Wrongly."

"Conviction survives error."

"Unfortunately."

Rinna saw me.

"Greg."

"I was summoned."

"You were invited."

"The child said bring my crutches."

"Did you?"

I looked down again.

Rinna did not smile.

Nessa did.

Jori said, "We need your walk."

That was more concerning.

"You can't have it."

"Borrow."

"Also suspicious."

Rinna pointed at the stage.

"We're changing the second act entrance."

I waited.

"And?"

"And the new sound pickup catches Marek's boots too hard."

Marek called, "My boots are expressive."

Hara said, "Your boots are loud."

"Same thing."

Rinna continued as if nobody had spoken.

"It also catches Jori's left brace when he crosses behind the wall."

I looked at Jori.

He tapped the leather-and-metal support strapped around his left wrist.

I had seen it before.

Never cared.

Apparently it clicked.

"And you want my crutches because..."

Nessa said, "Because if we fix the pickup for boots and a wrist brace, then discover tonight it hates crutch tips, Rinna will kill us."

Rinna said, "I will not kill anyone."

Nessa looked at me.

"See? Terrifying."

There it was.

Not a job.

Not magic training.

Not artifact appraisal.

They wanted my noise.

I could provide that.

"What pickup?"

Jori pointed toward house left.

Mounted beneath the front edge of the stage was a shallow wooden box with a brass face.

I had noticed similar boxes during the performance.

Wall-mounted sound devices.

Ordinary stage magic.

Useful enough that I had not thought about them while the play worked.

The best technology disappeared.

This one had apparently stopped disappearing.

Jori said, "Old second voice."

"That's a name?"

"Nickname."

"For what?"

"Sound return."

Rinna said, "It carries quiet stage speech forward without making the room sound shouted at."

That was a much better description.

I looked at the brass box.

"And now it likes boots."

"New pickup throat," Jori said.

He indicated the open wooden case.

"Original cracked last week. Replacement is close, not same."

"Registered?"

Rinna gave me a look.

I had become annoying in new directions.

Jori answered anyway.

"The main housing is registered theatre equipment. Throat is replaceable service part. This one's from North Lantern stock."

"Known compatible?"

"Known compatible with this housing family."

"Not this exact box."

"Correct."

I liked him.

"What changed?"

Jori said, "Speech carries. Foot contact carries too much."

Nessa added, "Chair legs. Prop table. One dropped spoon became the voice of God."

"I would pay to see that."

"You did not. We were rehearsing."

Marek called, "It was moving."

Hara said, "It was a spoon."

"Art resists categories."

Rinna closed her eyes briefly.

I understood why she had invited me.

Not because I was uniquely qualified.

Because I was available and came with a noise source they did not regularly have onstage.

Social accumulation could be humiliatingly practical.

Jori said, "Walk the new entrance. Normal pace."

"Where?"

He showed me.

Stage right behind the false wall, through the narrow opening, six steps across visible stage, stop at the painted table.

I looked at the route.

"That turn is tight."

Nessa said, "For you?"

"For anyone with two crutches and dignity."

"We can remove dignity."

"Already did theatre."

She nodded.

Fair.

The route was possible.

I set my crutches.

Walked.

Normal.

Tip.

Step.

Tip.

Step.

The brass box beneath the stage gave a faint hum.

Then my right crutch struck the board at the edge of the false wall.

The room answered.

THOCK.

Not loud exactly.

Large.

The sound seemed to come from the stage itself.

Marek applauded.

"Greg has arrived."

"Shut up."

Rinna said, "Again."

I did it again.

This time no wall strike.

Normal crutch contact still carried.

Not every placement.

Interesting.

One tip landed near the front quarter of the stage and produced a soft amplified knock.

Another three feet back did not.

I stopped.

"There."

Jori said, "I heard it."

He stepped onto the stage and tapped his heel along the same line.

Nothing.

Tapped again.

Nothing.

Nessa dragged a chair leg lightly.

The box hummed.

No carry.

Marek stamped.

Rinna said, "Don't."

He had already stamped.

The sound carried.

"Useful," he said.

"Obvious," Hara said.

Jori crouched near the front boards.

"Again, Greg. Same route."

I did.

One placement carried.

Different exact spot.

Same general strip.

Jori marked two chalk ticks.

Then asked Hara to speak from center stage.

She said, conversationally, "Marek is an idiot."

The room carried it beautifully.

Marek put a hand to his chest.

"Technology has betrayed me."

Rinna said, "Again."

Hara repeated the sentence.

Same.

Speech clean.

Jori moved the replacement throat's silver collar one notch.

Hara spoke again.

Softer.

Still clear.

Marek walked.

Boots quieter.

I crossed.

My crutches were quieter too.

"Done," I said.

Jori said, "No."

Of course.

He moved the collar another notch.

Hara spoke.

Her last word thinned strangely.

Not volume.

Shape.

The final consonant seemed to vanish.

Jori moved it back.

"There."

Rinna said, "So one notch from original."

"For this room today."

Good answer.

Rinna nodded.

No universal setting.

No solved forever.

Humidity, room load, throat condition, whatever else.

Jori asked me to cross twice more.

I did.

The pickup still caught one harder crutch placement near the front strip, but not ordinary walking.

"Acceptable?" Nessa asked.

Rinna looked at me.

"Can you walk the entrance without striking hard there?"

"Yes."

"Would someone unfamiliar with the stage?"

I considered.

"Probably not reliably."

She looked at Nessa.

"Then mark the strip backstage. Don't route visitors across it during live pickup."

Nessa nodded.

That was it.

No one redesigned the theatre around me.

No one declared the device unsafe.

They found the interaction and changed operating procedure.

I had become a test condition.

I was weirdly proud.

Marek said, "Can we keep Greg for the second act?"

Rinna said, "No."

"He has presence."

"He has errands."

I said, "Do I?"

Rinna looked at me.

"You should."

Fair.

Jori removed the throat again.

That surprised me.

"Thought you set it."

"I did."

"Then why remove it?"

"Because I want to check the seat before tonight."

He turned the brass tube in his hands.

The square collar had a faint dark line along one edge.

Not crack.

Maybe grime.

Maybe contact polish.

He held it beside the original cracked throat from the case.

The two were nearly identical.

Nearly.

The replacement's square collar was a fraction deeper.

I would not have noticed if they were not side by side.

"Close," I said.

"Close makes work," Jori replied.

That sentence belonged on every workshop wall in civilization.

He fitted the old cracked piece beside the new one.

"Original sat deeper?"

I asked.

"Shallower."

"Then new one is deeper."

"Yes."

"Wouldn't that seat farther?"

"If the housing were unworn."

There.

He pointed to the brass box under the stage.

"Seat's been dressed twice. New throat's nominally deeper, old housing is nominally less of a housing than it used to be."

I smiled.

"Technical term?"

"Extremely."

Nessa said, "Can I put that on the repair card?"

"No."

Rinna had moved to the edge of the stage.

"How long?"

Jori said, "Quarter hour."

"You said that before Greg came."

"Now I mean it."

"Dangerous."

She turned to me.

"Thank you."

"That's all?"

"Were you hoping for applause?"

"Money."

"No."

"Worth asking."

"You were here less than an hour."

"My rates are predatory."

"Your rate was invitation."

Also fair.

Then she said, "There may be paid house work next week. Two evenings. Not confirmed. If it happens, Nessa will ask you."

I paused.

There.

Not payment now.

Not promise.

Not conveniently thirty copper.

Just my name existing inside somebody's future staffing thought.

"What kind?"

"House route and late reset."

"Rate?"

"If it happens, you'll get the rate with the offer."

I nodded.

Healthy institution.

Marek called, "I can offer half a bit to insult Hara through the second voice."

Hara said, "You can do that for free."

"Market collapse."

I left before they recruited me into art.

Outside, Carrow had become bright.

Not warm.

Bright.

Clouds had broken enough that every wet roof edge threw light back at the street.

I stood under the theatre awning for a moment and adjusted my shoulder bag.

One copper.

No new money.

No artifact purchase.

No advancement.

Except that Rinna might think of me when paid house work appeared.

Jessa had thought of me when retired parts appeared.

Antonius had thought of me when a boundary check needed another pair of eyes.

Senna had drawn me and sold the drawing.

People were beginning to carry versions of me around when I wasn't present.

That was reputation, I supposed.

Terrible concept.

You could not supervise it.

I took the longer route home.

Not for philosophy.

Tanner's Cut was still partially closed.

The municipal board had been changed.

CHANNEL WORK

OLD DRAIN TRACE

EAST WALK OPEN

CARTS SINGLE FILE

The red stakes had moved.

The old diagonal drain was covered again except for two lifted sections farther south.

The green-coated specialist was not there.

Different workers.

Different day.

The service crawler sat on its spool cart, folded into itself like a dead brass centipede.

Definitely not alive.

Probably.

I crossed using the east walk.

The oval clicked at the ordinary street frame where two buildings nearly met overhead.

Normal.

No strange click.

Good.

At home, the rest stone was still in its box.

Good.

The holdfast was still on the high shelf.

Good.

Tavi's spoon carrier had gained a string.

Bad.

"What does the string do?" I asked.

"Pulls it."

He demonstrated.

The wooden spoon moved across the floor.

I stared.

"You made it functional."

"Yes."

"This is how civilization begins to fail."

He pulled it again.

One of the metal loops fell off.

Civilization recovered.

Lyssa asked, "Paid?"

"No."

"Six hours?"

"Less than one."

She looked impressed.

"You're learning."

"There may be paid work next week."

"May?"

"Two evenings. House route and reset. Not confirmed."

"Rate?"

"Not offered yet."

She nodded.

No fantasy arithmetic.

Good household.

I told her about the second voice.

Not every technical detail.

Enough.

Replacement throat.

Speech clean.

Boots too loud.

Crutches useful as an inconvenient test.

She laughed at that.

"You got invited because you're noisy."

"Specialized expertise."

"Should charge more."

"Rinna disagreed."

I went upstairs later.

The narrow scrap sat where I had left it.

Auction notes.

Mara rates.

Oval observations.

Hessa counts.

I considered adding something about the theatre pickup.

Replacement compatible by family but not exact.

Housing wear changed fit.

One-notch adjustment balanced speech against impact noise.

Useful.

But not mine.

Jori had the repair card.

The theatre had the equipment.

I had been a crutch-shaped variable.

I did not need to collect every fact I touched.

That was another kind of growth.

I put the scrap away.

Then noticed the rest-stone box.

One copper object.

Known fault.

No rated use.

I wanted to open it.

Not activate.

Just look.

I didn't.

That surprised me more than the theatre.

Maybe access was changing me in the useful direction.

When everything had been scarce, every open door felt temporary.

You rushed through before it closed.

Bought the thing.

Asked the question.

Took the work.

Used the chance.

Now there were enough doors that I could leave some unopened.

I hated that thought immediately.

Too wise.

I went downstairs before I wrote it down.

# CHAPTER 340

## THE MOVING LINE

Hessa's note arrived folded around a piece of string.

Not chalk this time.

String.

I distrusted variety.

The note said:

GREG

MIDDAY

SOUTH WALK

BOTH CRUTCHES

NO EQUIPMENT

HESSA

Underneath, smaller:

WEATHER IF STUPID

I read that twice.

Then looked out the window.

Carrow had produced one of those bright gray days where the clouds could not decide whether they were threatening rain or merely being dramatic.

Wind moved the laundry lines across the neighboring roofs.

Not hard.

Enough.

Lyssa looked up from the sleeve she was repairing.

"Hessa?"

"Apparently weather may be stupid."

"Then you'll fit in."

"Household support remains excellent."

I left the oval home.

That felt strange.

Not difficult.

Just noticeable.

The rest stone stayed boxed.

The holdfast stayed shelved.

One copper stayed in my purse because Hessa had specifically said no equipment, not no money, and I refused to become the sort of person who interpreted training notes as tax law.

South Walk turned out to be exactly what it sounded like.

A long covered stone arcade along the southern edge of an old civic yard.

Roof overhead.

Open arches on one side.

Wall on the other.

Wind came through anyway.

Of course it did.

Hessa was standing halfway down the arcade with a coil of thin white string, six chalk marks on the floor, and a shallow wooden tray.

No bead visible.

That was suspicious.

"You're late," she said.

"By how much?"

"You're not."

"Then that was hostile."

"Useful test."

"Of what?"

"Whether you'd waste time defending punctuality."

I stared at her.

"I did."

"Yes."

Terrible teacher.

I liked her.

The South Walk was empty except for us and one old man reading under the farthest arch.

He did not look up.

Good.

Hessa pointed at my bag.

"No equipment."

"Money."

"Keep it."

"Generous."

"Put the bag against the wall."

I did.

She looked at my crutches.

"Any shoulder issue from theatre?"

That got my attention.

"How do you know theatre?"

"You live in a city."

"That is not an answer."

"Any shoulder issue?"

"No. Ordinary fatigue. Fine today."

"Right leg?"

"Fine."

"Left knee?"

"Fine."

She nodded.

No ceremony.

No explanation for how she knew.

Annoying.

She pointed at the first two chalk marks.

"Minimal draws. Walking."

I blinked.

"Walking while drawing."

"Yes."

"Mana?"

"No. Decorative breathing."

"You could be clearer."

"I could."

There were six chalk marks in a straight line, each perhaps four normal steps apart.

For me, more complicated.

Crutch.

Right foot.

Crutch.

Swing.

Set.

Not exactly that every time.

The body found its own sequence when I stopped naming it.

Hessa took the string.

Tied one end loosely to a small brass ring set into the wall.

The other end she held.

The string crossed the walkway at waist height, then sagged slightly.

"What's that for?"

"Later."

"I hate later."

"Walk."

I started at the first chalk mark.

Minimal draw.

No shaping.

No external effect.

Just the smallest supervised pull I had been doing since Hessa decided my instincts were a civic hazard.

First attempt.

Clean enough.

Second chalk mark.

I moved.

Drew again while the left crutch was forward.

The draw came rough.

Not failed.

Uneven.

Third.

I timed it after right-foot contact.

Cleaner.

Fourth.

Mid-swing.

Bad.

The draw broke almost immediately.

Hessa said nothing.

Fifth.

I waited for both crutches to settle.

Clean.

Sixth.

Tried before the right foot landed.

Thin and ugly.

Hessa raised one finger.

"Stop."

I stopped.

"Six," she said.

I nodded.

Current total forty-seven minimal draws.

I would write it later.

Probably.

Maybe.

No, I would.

Counts mattered here.

"What changed?" Hessa asked.

"Body contact."

"Too broad."

"When I draw during a stable contact phase, cleaner. During swing or transition, worse."

"Which contact?"

I thought.

"Not sure. Both crutches planted was best. Right foot planted also good. Left-side body loading feels different because there is no foot contact."

"Good."

Not praise.

Confirmation.

She moved the tray into view.

Inside was the same plain white ceramic bead from Chapter Three Hundred Thirty-Three.

I recognized it.

"You kept it."

"It belongs to me."

"Emotionally?"

"Legally."

"Colder."

She set the bead on the floor near the wall.

"No external effect yet. Shaping only."

Nine deliberate shaping attempts.

She made me walk between the chalk marks while holding a tiny bounded shape at different points in my gait.

Not far.

Not clever.

No target.

Just sustain something while the body moved underneath it.

First three were awful.

One collapsed when my right crutch landed harder than expected.

One overcorrected because I felt the shape thin while my shoulders shifted.

One lasted almost two steps, then smeared when I changed pace.

Hessa said, "You're treating every body change as mana change."

"It feels like mana change."

"Of course it does."

"Helpful."

"Your body is moving. Your perception is moving with it. The field may not be doing what you think."

That landed.

Not profound.

Annoyingly practical.

Attempts four through six improved when she made me choose one gait phase and only shape there.

Crutches planted.

Draw.

Hold.

Move.

Release.

Again.

The fourth held for half a breath.

Fifth held a little longer.

Sixth was clean enough that Hessa said, "Again differently."

Of course.

Seven through nine she made me shape during movement instead of at stable contact.

Not full continuous control.

Just start after push-off and release before landing.

Attempt seven failed almost immediately.

Eight held, thin.

Nine held through one full body transition without the late shove.

I smiled.

Hessa said, "Don't."

"I'm allowed facial expressions."

"Not that one."

"Which one?"

"The one before you chase a result."

I stopped smiling.

Rude.

Accurate.

Nine deliberate shaping attempts.

Total fifty.

That number felt suspiciously clean.

I refused to care.

Hessa picked up the bead.

Then the string finally became relevant.

She tied the bead to it.

Not through the bead.

The bead sat in a tiny cloth loop that hung from the string like a bad ornament.

"Moving target," I said.

"Not yet."

She pulled the free end of the string.

The bead slid along the waist-high line from one wall point toward her.

Slow.

Very slow.

"That's a moving target."

"The target moves. You do not."

"Pedantic."

"Necessary."

She reset the bead near the wall.

"External effect. One grain toward you. Same magnitude as last session when possible."

"On the hanging bead?"

"Yes."

"While it moves?"

"Eventually."

First she left it still.

Attempt one.

Near enough.

Tiny movement.

Hard to judge because hanging bead wanted to swing.

Hessa had anticipated that.

Of course.

The cloth loop touched a second lower thread that acted as a guide.

Not rigid.

Enough to limit side swing.

"Again."

Attempt two.

Clean one-grain-ish movement along the string.

"Good."

Then she moved the bead.

Slow slide.

Attempt three.

No contact.

Attempt four.

I reached too late.

The bead had moved through the place I was holding in my head.

Attempt five.

I chased it.

Bad.

The effect smeared behind the bead like I was trying to push yesterday.

Hessa stopped the string.

"What are you targeting?"

"The bead."

"No."

"The moving bead."

"Still no."

I stared.

She waited.

I hated teachers who waited.

"A predicted position?"

"Better."

"The point where the bead will be when the effect lands."

"Better."

"That's the same thing."

"It isn't."

Unfortunately, she was right.

A target was not always an object.

Sometimes it was an intersection between object and time.

I did not say that out loud because it sounded like something I'd write on a wall and regret.

Attempt six.

Slow bead.

I targeted ahead.

Too far.

Nothing.

Attempt seven.

Ahead, less.

The bead twitched.

Maybe one grain.

Hessa said, "Count it as contact, not clean."

Good.

Attempt eight.

Clean.

Tiny movement toward me while the bead was already moving sideways along the string.

The vectors made my brain itch.

The effect did not stop the slide.

Just added a little inward bias.

"There," I said.

"Yes."

She kept moving the bead.

Attempt nine.

Failed.

Attempt ten.

Clean enough.

Attempt eleven.

Overstrong.

Two or three grains equivalent.

Hessa's face changed.

Not alarm.

Disapproval.

"You corrected late."

"I felt it miss."

"And then?"

"Pushed."

"Why?"

"Because I wanted contact."

"Yes."

There it was again.

Greed.

Not money.

Result greed.

The same ugly little instinct wearing different clothes.

Attempt twelve.

She slowed the bead further.

I let one pass without acting.

That surprised me.

"You skipped it," Hessa said.

"Bad timing."

"Good."

Then on the next pass I acted.

Clean small movement.

She stopped the bead.

"Twelve external effects."

I nodded.

Total one hundred three.

Forty-seven minimal.

Fifty deliberate shaping.

One hundred three external.

I repeated them silently.

Hessa untied the bead.

I thought we were done.

Wrong.

She moved the chalk marks.

Not all of them.

Three.

She placed one near the wall, one halfway across the arcade, one under the outer arch where wind moved through.

Then put the bead on the floor at the middle mark.

"Now you move. Target does not."

I exhaled.

"You enjoy separating axes."

"You enjoy combining them and naming the failure one thing."

Fair.

No new external attempt yet.

She first had me walk past the bead three times without mana.

Just route.

Crutches.

Right foot.

Body angle.

Where I looked.

Where the bead entered and left peripheral vision.

The first pass I watched it too hard and clipped my right crutch tip against a chalk edge.

No fall.

Just ugly contact.

Hessa said, "Again."

Second pass I looked ahead.

Better.

Third pass I let the bead live in peripheral vision.

Best.

"Why?" she asked.

"Because if I stare down, I ruin walking geometry."

"And?"

"I don't need visual fixation to hold target position."

"Do you know that?"

I paused.

"No. I suspect it."

"Better."

We did not add counts because no magic.

Then she allowed three more deliberate shaping attempts while walking past the stationary bead.

I objected.

"You said nine shaping attempts."

"I said nothing of the kind."

She had not.

I had counted nine because she stopped after nine.

Annoying.

So total would change again.

Attempt ten shaping.

Failed during turn.

Eleven.

Held through one pass.

Twelve.

Held while I did not look directly at bead.

Interesting.

Total deliberate shaping fifty-three.

Then Hessa said, "No external effect."

I looked at her.

"Why set this up?"

"To see if you would ask."

"I am asking."

"Good."

"And?"

"You're done."

I stared at the bead.

Static.

Right there.

I wanted one attempt.

Badly.

Not because it mattered.

Because the experiment had created the question.

Could I move while affecting a stationary target?

Everything in me wanted the answer immediately.

I looked at Hessa.

"One attempt?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you did enough today to create a cleaner question."

"And now we stop before answering it."

"Yes."

"Cruel."

"Useful."

I looked down the arcade.

The old man at the far arch turned a page.

The weather had remained stupid but not malicious.

Wind pushed a little rain through the open side and dotted the stone near the outer chalk mark.

Hessa picked up the bead.

"What did you learn?"

"Moving target is not the same as far target."

"Obvious."

"Body movement changes my perception of control."

"Better."

"Stable contact phases are easier for minimal draw than swing phases."

"Today."

"Today."

"Good."

"I chase moving targets by correcting behind them."

"Yes."

"Prediction matters."

"Maybe."

"You hate declarative statements."

"Only unsupported ones."

"That's most of mine."

"Yes."

I considered.

"I can produce a small clean effect on a slowly moving target under supervision."

"More precise."

"At near range."

"Good."

"Using one-grain-ish magnitude."

"Good."

"With at least two clean or clean-enough contacts today."

"Three, depending how strict you are."

"You decide counts."

"No. You record your standard."

I hated responsibility.

"And we did not test me moving with external effect."

"Correct."

"Deliberately."

"Correct."

"Because you are evil."

"Unrelated."

I smiled.

She did not tell me to stop.

Progress.

Then she said, "One more thing."

Of course.

She pointed at my crutches.

"When you felt the shape thin during movement, where did you correct from?"

I thought back.

Shoulder?

Hands?

Mana sense?

"Usually after the right crutch loaded."

"Why?"

"Because that's when I felt the drop most clearly."

"Or when your upper body changed most clearly."

There.

Not conclusion.

A split.

Mana instability versus body-sensation contamination.

"How do we separate those?"

"Later."

I hated later.

She handed me the piece of string from the note.

"What's this for?"

"You can keep it."

"Why?"

"So you stop thinking every object I hand you is instruction."

I looked at the string.

Plain white cord.

Nothing magical.

Probably.

"This is psychological warfare."

"Go home, Greg."

I went home.

Not directly.

The rain had strengthened enough to make the shortest route unpleasant, so I took the covered market arcade.

Different route.

More people.

A fruit seller had moved half her table three feet inward because water was coming through one broken gutter.

A courier ran past with a waxed shoulder cape.

Two children were floating folded paper boats down the curb channel and arguing over whether touching a boat disqualified the race.

Normal city magic remained absent.

Normal city nonsense did not.

I liked that.

At home, Tavi immediately saw the string.

"Mine?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Hessa gave it to me."

"Magic?"

"No."

"Then mine?"

Strong market pressure.

I considered.

The point, apparently, was to stop treating every object as instruction.

Giving it to Tavi might count.

"You may have half."

"Why half?"

"Reserve."

He accepted this immediately, which was concerning.

I cut the string.

He took his half to the spoon carrier.

Of course.

Lyssa asked, "How was training?"

"I learned walking is harder when I think about walking."

"Advanced."

"Also moving targets are inconvenient."

"Very advanced."

Cala called from the kitchen, "Did he fall?"

"No."

"Then good."

Household standards remained excellent.

I went upstairs with my half of the string.

The oval was where I had left it.

The rest stone box remained closed.

The holdfast sat on its high shelf.

Three artifacts.

None relevant.

That felt healthy.

I took out the narrow scrap.

Counts first.

41 / 41 / 91 became:

47 MINIMAL

53 DELIBERATE SHAPING

103 EXTERNAL EFFECT

Then I added:

MOVEMENT CHANGES CONTROL PROBLEM

Crossed out CONTROL PROBLEM.

Too broad.

Wrote:

BODY MOTION + MANA PERCEPTION MAY BE MIXED

Then:

STABLE CONTACT PHASE EASIER TODAY

Then:

MOVING TARGET: STOP CHASING WHERE IT WAS

I stared at that one.

Too wise again.

I almost crossed it out.

Did not.

Useful enough.

Then:

NO TEST YET: GREG MOVING + EXTERNAL EFFECT

That was the clean future question.

Not answer.

Question.

I put the scrap away.

The one copper stayed in my purse.

The rest stone stayed boxed.

Possible theatre work stayed possible.

Magic stayed supervised.

For once, the number of unfinished things in my life did not feel like failure.

It felt like inventory.

I disliked that thought less than expected.

# CHAPTER 341

## THE LONG MILE

Cala's cousin arrived with a cart, a cracked wheel rim, and a problem that lived eight miles outside Carrow.

I knew this because he announced all three before breakfast.

"Cart's fine," he said.

The cart outside leaned visibly to the left.

"Wheel's cracked."

"You just said the cart was fine."

"Cart is. Wheel isn't."

I looked at Cala.

She was stirring something in a pot and refusing to help me.

The man was called Beren.

I had met him once.

Maybe twice.

Wide face, short beard, shoulders built by carrying things he should have asked other people to carry.

He lived north of Carrow near a place called Moss Ford, which was less a village than several farms, a mill, a shrine, and enough houses to make arguments communal.

His cart carried three sacks of grain, a wrapped iron fitting, two empty baskets, and a wooden cage containing a bird that hated civilization.

The bird screamed at me.

"Good morning," I said.

It screamed again.

"Strong position."

Beren ignored the bird.

"Need a second pair of eyes."

I looked behind me.

"Cala?"

"You."

"I have one pair."

"You know field marks."
