That was dangerous language.

"I have looked at some field marks."

"Same thing."

"Very much not."

Cala finally turned.

"Tell him the whole thing."

Beren sighed like the whole thing had personally inconvenienced him.

"North path's moved."

"Paths do that."

"Not that one."

"Then what moved?"

"Don't know."

Excellent beginning.

He explained.

There was an old public foot-and-cart route between Moss Ford and Carrow's north road.

Not the main road.

A secondary route.

Used by farm carts, walkers, mill traffic when the lower road flooded, and people avoiding toll weight on larger loads.

Three days ago, the path began bending east around a low meadow.

Not people choosing a new track.

The actual packed road.

"Bending how?"

"You walk straight. End up east."

"That is usually how turns work."

"No turn."

I stopped being funny.

"No visible turn?"

"No."

"Landmarks?"

"Wrong."

"How wrong?"

"Mill stack looks closer. Shrine stones look farther. Creek crossing comes early."

There.

Interesting.

"Anyone hurt?"

"No."

"Missing?"

"No."

"Carts getting stuck?"

"One went into wet ground yesterday because driver followed the fence instead of road."

"Why would he do that?"

"Because road put him east of his own fence."

That was better.

Or worse.

"Who has checked it?"

"Moss shrine keeper. Mill people. Two road men came yesterday, put stakes, argued, left."

"What did they say?"

"Don't use after dark."

"Useful."

"Need to bring this fitting to my brother anyway. Thought you might come look."

I looked at Cala.

"Why me?"

She shrugged.

"You like things that are wrong."

That was unfairly accurate.

Beren added, "And you know the old field stuff."

"Some."

"And you're cheap."

"Ah."

There it was.

"How cheap?"

"Lunch. Ride there and back."

"That's not pay."

"No."

"Good. I wanted the insult clearly labeled."

He grinned.

I considered saying no.

That was new enough to notice.

Eight miles out.

Ride both ways.

No pay.

Unknown road effect.

No one missing or injured.

Road workers already aware.

I had no obligation.

I could stay home.

I could go to the market.

I could stare responsibly at my boxed dangerous stone.

I could do nothing.

Then the bird screamed.

"Fine," I said.

Cala said, "Because of the bird?"

"It made a compelling argument."

I packed water, narrow scrap, charcoal, and nothing magical except the oval.

I almost left that too.

Then reconsidered.

A route behaving incorrectly was one of the few contexts where the oval's known behavior might actually be relevant.

Not authoritative.

Relevant.

The holdfast stayed home.

The rest stone stayed boxed.

No reason to bring either.

Beren's cart wheel had a split iron rim brace, not a cracked wheel rim exactly.

The wood itself was fine.

He had lied by simplification.

Normal human behavior.

I sat on the left side of the cart bed with my crutches tied beside me where I could reach them.

Not comfortable.

Better than walking eight miles before investigating a road that might not respect distance.

We left through North Gate.

The oval clicked.

Normal.

Carrow thinned slowly.

Stone buildings became timber.

Timber became yards.

Yards became fields.

The road widened first, then narrowed.

We passed two freight carts, a woman driving six goats, and a boy carrying a bundle of reeds taller than he was.

Nothing magical happened.

This was disappointing in the healthy way.

Beren talked.

Mostly about grain prices without numbers.

Bad rain.

Good rain.

Wrong rain.

Mill timing.

His brother's inability to repair anything before it became expensive.

The bird screamed whenever he paused.

I learned its name was Hush.

"You named the loud bird Hush."

"Daughter did."

"Good child."

Two miles out, the land began to roll.

Not hills.

Long shallow rises.

Fields separated by stone walls and drainage cuts.

Magic lived here differently than in Carrow.

Less brass.

Less glass.

More things tied to posts.

Small white stones hung from orchard branches on red cord.

A field gate had three copper nails driven into the top beam in a triangle.

At one drainage crossing, a flat black slab sat half submerged with water bending around it too cleanly.

I knew categories.

Weather wards.

Boundary discouragement.

Flow shaping.

Local implementations varied.

I did not ask about every one.

This was also growth.

At the fourth mile marker, Beren pointed.

"Road men."

Two fresh stakes stood beside the road.

White tops.

Black numbers.

One on each side.

A thin cord had been stretched between them earlier, judging by the cut grass, but removed now.

"Yesterday?"

"Yes."

"Where does the problem start?"

"Past next rise."

We continued.

The road looked ordinary.

Packed dirt.

Wheel ruts.

Grass shoulders.

Fence to the west.

Low meadow east.

Mill stack ahead-left.

A line of dark trees farther north.

Then Beren said, "There."

I felt nothing.

No mana pressure.

No vertigo.

No dramatic shimmer.

The road continued straight.

The cart continued straight.

The fence continued straight.

The mill stack moved right.

I frowned.

Not physically.

Relative position.

We had not turned.

The stack had shifted from ahead-left toward directly ahead.

I looked back.

The rise behind us seemed slightly farther west than it should have been.

"Stop."

Beren stopped.

The full cart weight settled.

Hush screamed.

I untied my crutches and climbed down.

Ground ordinary.

Packed road beneath right foot and crutch tips.

I turned slowly.

Fence west.

Meadow east.

Road straight.

Except the wheel ruts ahead curved very slightly east.

No.

Not curved.

They were straight.

The fence and ruts were no longer parallel.

That was the wrongness.

"Was fence always like that?"

Beren said, "Fence is old."

"Not my question."

"I don't know."

Good answer eventually.

I walked ten yards ahead.

Oval in bag.

No click.

Another ten.

No click.

At a pair of old stone posts with no gate between them, click.

Normal qualifying threshold.

The oval turned back toward the posts.

Good.

I kept walking.

Twenty yards.

The mill stack shifted farther right.

I looked back at the stone posts.

They appeared behind-left.

The oval pointed behind me.

Toward the posts.

As expected.

I turned my body without moving.

Oval still toward posts.

Good.

Beren called, "Anything?"

"Road is rude."

"Knew that."

I returned to the cart.

Crossed posts.

Click.

Oval updated normally.

No strange behavior.

That was information.

Not much.

"Take me to the shrine stones."

"They're ahead."

"Apparently everything is."

We continued slowly.

The road did something I disliked.

Distance accumulated normally under the wheels.

Landmarks did not.

A drainage ditch that should have crossed after a long meadow arrived within perhaps half the expected travel.

Beren swore.

"Early."

"You said that."

"Worse today."

I looked at him.

"You sure?"

He hesitated.

"No."

Good.

We crossed the ditch.

The water flowed west to east.

Normal enough.

The road beyond remained dry.

Then the meadow grass changed.

Not color.

Direction.

Every blade leaned north despite wind moving south.

I got down again.

Beren said, "Don't wander."

"Wasn't planning to."

I approached the shoulder but stayed on packed ground.

Grass leaned north.

Wind touched my face from north to south.

The grass moved against it.

Not all grass.

A band perhaps six feet wide running diagonally across the meadow.

I followed the band with my eyes.

It crossed the road fifty yards ahead.

There.

Something physical enough to see once I knew where to look.

"You see that?"

Beren squinted.

"Grass?"

"The strip."

He stared longer.

"Oh."

"Was that there yesterday?"

"Didn't see it."

Again, not same as no.

We did not cross it yet.

I took out the narrow scrap.

Drew rough road.

Fence.

Meadow.

Ditch.

Grass band.

No conclusions.

Then a cart approached from the north.

Empty flatbed.

Older woman driving.

She slowed when she saw us stopped.

"Road open?"

Beren called, "Supposedly."

She snorted.

"Supposedly isn't a road condition."

I liked her.

"You came through the strange section?" I asked.

"If you mean the long mile, yes."

There.

A name.

"Long mile?"

"That's what my father called it when it woke up."

Beren turned toward her.

"Woke up?"

She looked at him like he was young.

He was at least forty.

"You Moss Ford people forgot everything useful."

"I'm Moss Ford people."

"Exactly."

I stepped closer but stayed clear of her horse.

"How often?"

She shrugged.

"Not in my adult life. Father said twice when he was young. Grandmother said more."

"What happens?"

"Road goes long."

"It feels shorter in places."

"Depends which way."

Interesting.

"Dangerous?"

"If you're stupid."

Universal condition.

"Specific stupid?"

She pointed toward the meadow.

"Leave the packed road because your eyes tell you the fence is closer than the road, you get wet. Follow a landmark instead of the ruts, you get lost. Travel after dark, maybe worse."

"Maybe?"

"I wasn't there."

Good.

"What causes it?"

"Road."

"Very helpful."

"You're welcome."

Beren asked, "Why didn't shrine keeper say this?"

"Which keeper?"

He named someone I did not know.

She made a face.

"His mother's from south."

Apparently magical road knowledge was hereditary through local gossip.

Plausible.

I asked, "Grass strip?"

She looked.

"Don't cross off-road there."

"Why?"

"Because I said so."

"Excellent evidence."

She smiled.

"You can learn without obeying. Once."

Fair.

"Does the road itself cross it safely?"

"Usually."

"Usually today or usually historically?"

That made her pause.

"Good question."

She looked at the ruts.

Then the grass.

"I crossed. Horse didn't care."

Better evidence.

"How long ago?"

"Quarter hour."

Useful.

She drove on.

No name exchanged.

No destiny.

Just somebody who knew a road.

Beren watched her leave.

"My father never mentioned long mile."

"Maybe he didn't like you."

"He didn't."

That took the joke away.

"Sorry."

"Why? He was an ass."

Good.

We approached the grass band on the road.

Slow.

I kept the oval hanging loose inside my open bag where I could see orientation.

Not touching it.

Not obeying it.

At the edge of the band, no click.

We crossed.

The world did not shimmer.

The cart did not vanish.

Hush screamed.

Normal.

Then the oval rotated.

Not back toward the grass band.

Not toward the last stone posts.

It turned ninety degrees toward the meadow.

I stared.

No click.

Beren said, "What?"

"Stop."

He stopped.

The oval held east.

Toward open meadow.

I stepped down.

Did not leave road.

Turned the bag.

Oval remained room-relative.

East.

Not toward any visible door or frame.

No click.

That was new.

Or not new exactly.

It had oriented without crossings before.

Theatre.

Building fire.

Window.

But here there was no obvious threshold nearby.

Unless the grass band counted.

Except it had not clicked.

And it pointed along the band, not back across it.

Maybe.

I checked alignment.

The leaning grass strip ran northeast-southwest.

Oval pointed almost due east.

Not clean.

Good.

Messy evidence was safer.

"What is it?" Beren asked.

"Object I don't understand."

"Why bring it?"

"Because I make good decisions."

He accepted that for some reason.

I watched for a full minute.

Oval slowly drifted.

Not much.

East toward southeast.

Then stopped.

The mill stack, visible beyond meadow, seemed to slide left while we stood still.

I blinked.

Still left.

"Beren."

"Yeah."

"Is the mill moving?"

"No."

"Good."

"Looks like it."

"Less good."

We remained on the road.

After perhaps another minute, the apparent mill position stabilized.

The oval turned back.

Not toward our last threshold.

Toward the road behind us.

Then clicked.

Once.

I did not like that.

"We're continuing," I said.

Beren looked surprised.

"Thought you'd want to poke it."

"I'm becoming boring."

"Shame."

We continued.

The next half mile took long enough that I understood the old woman's name for it.

Not physically long.

Maybe.

My body said we traveled normal distance.

Cart wheels turned.

Horse walked.

Time passed.

But the shrine stones ahead refused to get closer for several minutes.

Then they arrived almost all at once.

Three waist-high stones beside the road.

Each capped with a little iron roof.

Blue thread tied between them.

A man waited there.

Gray robe.

Bare head despite drizzle.

Probably shrine keeper.

He looked annoyed before we spoke.

"You came from Carrow?"

Beren said, "Yes."

"Road men sent word they're closing this section by evening."

"Why?"

"Because people keep leaving the road."

There it was.

Not because the road itself was killing anyone.

Because people responded badly to distorted geometry.

The keeper pointed to fresh white stakes along both shoulders.

"Stay between marks. Moss Ford side open until dusk. Carrow side may close earlier if they get enough cord."

I asked, "Long mile?"

He looked at me.

"Who told you that?"

"Woman with empty flatbed."

"Old Nera."

A name now.

Fine.

"She says it woke before."

He sighed.

"Everyone says something now."

"Do records?"

That got a better look.

"Shrine book has two entries. Maybe three. Last clear one fifty-eight years ago."

There.

"What do they say?"

"Not enough."

"Can I read them?"

"No."

Healthy boundary.

"Summary?"

"Road-length distortion. Landmark disagreement. Do not leave marked path. Stronger after sunset. Settled within six days first entry, eleven second. Third might be same event copied twice."

Excellent.

"Cause?"

"No agreed cause in book."

Even better.

Beren said, "Why didn't you tell us yesterday?"

Keeper looked at him.

"Yesterday I was looking for the book."

Reasonable.

"Road men know now?"

"They have the summary."

Good institution eventually.

I looked at the shrine stones.

Blue thread between them trembled despite almost no wind here.

"Those monitoring?"

"Boundary witness."

"For the road?"

"For shrine ground."

Not everything was about current mystery.

Useful correction.

"Any change?"

"No."

So whatever the long mile was doing, shrine boundary had not visibly shifted according to its local witness system.

Maybe.

I did not know how that system worked.

I asked no more.

Beren delivered his wrapped fitting to a man at the mill who looked exactly enough like him to be his brother and differently enough to explain family arguments.

We ate lunch there.

Bread.

Hard cheese.

Something pickled.

Hush got grain and became quiet for the first time all day.

"Dead?" I asked.

Beren checked.

"Eating."

"Same appearance."

His brother asked why I had come.

Beren said, "He likes wrong things."

Apparently that was becoming my profession.

We returned before dusk.

Road workers had added cord between the white stakes.

Not magical cord as far as I could tell.

Bright enough to see.

A worker at the Moss Ford side told us not to stop inside the marked section unless necessary and absolutely not leave the packed surface.

We followed instructions.

The return felt different.

The shrine stones vanished behind us too quickly.

The drainage ditch took too long to arrive.

The mill stack appeared on the wrong side of my shoulder for a while.

The oval stayed wrapped in my bag.

I did not watch it.

That was deliberate.

If the road was safe enough under marked procedure, I wanted to know what the road felt like without turning the artifact into a compass I did not understand.

We crossed the grass band.

Nothing happened that I could verify.

We passed the old stone posts.

Click.

Normal.

Then the fourth-mile stakes.

Road ordinary again.

Not suddenly.

Just gradually less rude.

By North Gate, I was tired despite riding most of the day.

Watching distance misbehave was exhausting.

Carrow looked offensively stable.

Buildings stayed where architecture had put them.

I appreciated this.

Beren dropped me at home.

"Thanks."

"For what?"

"Coming."

"I solved nothing."

"Didn't ask you to."

That was surprisingly nice.

He handed me a small sack.

I looked inside.

Onions.

I stared at him.

"Cala said you bought good ones."

"This is payment?"

"No."

"Good."

"My brother grows them."

"Still suspicious."

He left.

Cala was delighted by the onions.

Of course.

I had traveled sixteen miles to return with produce.

Life had structure.

Later, upstairs, I took out the narrow scrap.

I wrote:

NORTH ROAD / MOSS FORD SECONDARY ROUTE

LOCAL NAME: LONG MILE

CURRENT: LANDMARK / DISTANCE DISAGREEMENT

ROAD ITSELF PASSABLE TODAY IF STAYING MARKED

PEOPLE LEAVING ROAD = CURRENT PRACTICAL DANGER

SHRINE BOOK: 2 CLEAR PRIOR EVENTS, MAYBE 3 / LAST CLEAR 58 YRS / 6 DAYS + 11 DAYS / STRONGER AFTER SUNSET / CAUSE UNAGREED

Then oval.

AT GRASS BAND CROSSING: NO CLICK / ORIENTED EAST THEN SE / LATER TURNED BACK + CLICK WHILE STATIONARY

I stared at that.

Could be important.

Could be useless.

Could be the oval responding to route distortion.

Could be the oval failing because route geometry itself was unstable.

Could be neither.

I added:

DO NOT USE AS NAVIGATION THERE.

That felt important enough to underline once.

Not twice.

Then I stopped.

The road men had a procedure.

The shrine had old records.

Nera had inherited memory.

Beren had local experience but not the old name.

I had one confused artifact observation.

Nobody possessed the whole thing.

That was normal.

Maybe more normal than knowing.

Downstairs, Tavi shouted.

"GREG!"

I went down.

He had tied Hessa's string to the spoon carrier and looped the other end around a chair leg.

"Look."

He pulled.

The carrier moved until the string tightened around the chair.

Then stopped.

"It can't go farther," he said.

"Tragic."

"Need longer string."

I thought about the long mile.

"Sometimes that makes it worse."

Tavi stared at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

Good.

I was still capable of not teaching children accidental philosophy.

# CHAPTER 342

## THE HOUSE LINE

The theatre finally offered me money.

This happened while I was eating one of Beren's onions.

Not raw.

I had standards.

Cala had put them into a pan with something salty and enough fat to make agriculture worthwhile.

Nessa knocked once, entered after Cala answered, and held out a folded paper.

"Rinna said if I let you negotiate in person, we'd lose daylight."

"That is insulting."

"She knows you."

Fair.

I took the paper.

Two evenings.

House route and late reset.

Second bell through clear.

Two copper each evening.

Payment after clear.

No meal.

No standing call.

There it was.

Actual work.

Actual rate.

Four copper total if I did both nights.

Not thirty-six.

Not destiny.

Still useful.

"Tonight?"

"Tonight and three nights from now."

"Why three?"

"Because that's when the show is."

Good system.

"What does house route mean?"

"Front door to seats. Keep aisles clear. Help late seating. Watch the left side after first bell because the flower frame makes that route stupid."

"And reset?"

"After house clear, chairs, aisle markers, loose programs, anything audience moved that shouldn't stay moved. Jori handles stage. We handle house."

"How late?"

"Depends how slowly rich people leave."

"Dangerous employment condition."

"You want it?"

I read the paper again.

Two copper tonight.

One copper currently in my purse.

Three after the shift if nobody invented deductions.

Five after the second evening if both happened as offered and I did not spend anything in between.

I did not calculate what five copper could buy.

Not immediately.

That was growth.

Then I calculated it.

Mara housing observation.

Public counter options.

Reserve afterward.

Fine.

Partial growth.

"Yes."

Nessa held out her hand.

"Paper."

"I thought I kept it."

"It's the house copy. I need your mark."

"I have a mark now?"

"You can write your name."

Less exciting.

I signed.

She tore the bottom strip along a scored line and gave that to me.

GREG / HOUSE ROUTE + LATE RESET / TONIGHT + THIRD NIGHT / 2 C EACH / PAY AFTER CLEAR

Useful.

No ambiguity.

Cala read it over my shoulder.

"Good."

Lyssa asked, "How many hours?"

Nessa said, "Usually four. Maybe five."

Lyssa looked at me.

"See?"

"I didn't volunteer."

"You're improving."

Nessa left.

I finished the onions.

Work started before second bell.

The theatre looked different from the front doors when the doors mattered.

I had entered through them as audience.

I had carried things through side routes.

I had watched people become a crowd after they were already seated.

I had not watched the crowd form.

That was a different organism.

Nessa gave me a narrow gray sash.

"House."

I tied it around my upper arm.

"Do I get authority?"

"No."

"Then what does sash do?"

"Makes people ask you questions."

"Terrible artifact."

"Not magical."

"Worse."

Front table sat just inside the doors.

Two workers handled names, paid seats, comp marks, and program stacks.

I knew neither well.

One was an older man with a narrow mustache and a voice designed to say no politely.

The other was a young woman who could count tickets while answering three questions and never looked impressed by anyone.

Good people.

My route started after them.

Front doors.

Short entry aisle.

Split.

House left or house right.

My responsibility mostly left.

Third-row-back aisle seat still existed.

Not mine tonight.

Work had displaced sentiment immediately.

Healthy.

Nessa walked me through the route.

"Before first bell, normal seating. After first bell, no center crossing during scene one. Left late entry holds at back wall until cue."

"What cue?"

"Blue lamp under rail goes dark."

I looked.

Small blue lamp tucked beneath side rail.

"And then?"

"Move whoever is waiting down the left aisle during the scene change. Quiet. If they argue, don't argue back."

"What do I do?"

"Get me."

Excellent procedure.

"Flower frame?"

"Act two. It stages through left rear. Nobody stands in that pocket after second act starts."

She pointed at a shallow rectangular area near the rear wall marked with two chalk corners.

"That's the frame path."

"Audience can see it?"

"Not when they're where they belong."

Theatre summarized.

"What about crutches?"

"Your crutches?"

"No, the crowd's."

She looked at me.

I smiled.

"Mine. Is there anywhere they catch?"

"You've walked it."

"Not with thirty people moving around me."

That made her stop.

Good.

We walked it again.

Different question.

At the left split, a decorative rope post narrowed the turn.

Fine alone.

Stupid if somebody stopped there.

Nessa moved the rope post six inches.

At row seven, one chair sat slightly proud of the others.

My right crutch could pass.

A wider body with a coat probably could too.

Two people passing opposite would not.

Nessa pushed it in.

Small changes.

No speech.

House geometry.

"Anything else?" she asked.

I looked.

"Back wall hold needs a clear edge. If three late people stand there, fourth blocks the door."

"They usually stack two."

"Usually isn't tonight."

She looked at me.

"Why?"

I shrugged.

"No reason."

Then someone outside shouted about the north road.

We both turned.

Not theatre voice.

Street voice.

A man at the front table was explaining something to the mustached worker.

"North side carts are coming through East Cut now. Gate board says secondary road closed."

There.

Long mile residue.

Not my mystery.

Still part of the city.

Nessa asked, "That affect us?"

The young ticket woman said, "If people planned North Gate, they'll be late."

Mustache said, "Or not come."

Both plausible.

Rinna appeared from somewhere behind civilization.

"What happened?"

They told her.

She did not look at me.

Good.

I was not North Road authority because I had ridden it once.

Rinna said, "Keep first bell on time. Hold late seats by normal cue. If more than six waiting, open right rear too after scene one."

Nessa said, "Right rear is clear?"

"Check it."

They moved.

I remained where assigned.

Also growth.

I did not volunteer my road story.

Doors opened properly a little later.

People arrived in patterns.

Pairs first.

Older couples.

A family with three children and one child who appeared to be operating independently of the family.

Two young men who had clearly decided clothing could substitute for manners.

A woman with a cane.

A broad man carrying a folded coat over one arm despite wearing another coat.

Why.

People asked questions because of the gray sash.

Nessa had been truthful.

"House left?"

"This way."

"Row five?"

"Past the rail, second opening."

"Is there a privy?"

"Front hall right."

"Can I take wine in?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I enjoy denying joy."

He stared.

I pointed at the sign.

"Also that."

He laughed and finished the cup outside.

Good outcome.

The woman with the cane had row eight, center-left.

She looked at the aisle.

Then at the chair.

Then at me.

"Does that row have another exit?"

"Right end."

"Closer?"

"From your seat, no."

She nodded.

No request.

No problem.

I did not rearrange the world because she owned a cane.

She walked in and sat.

Simple.

First bell approached.

House filled.

Not full.

Healthy crowd.

Then the north-side people arrived.

Not six.

Eleven in the first group.

Then four more.

Then a family of five arguing about whose fault the route change was.

The entry hall thickened.

Front table slowed.

Not chaos.

Compression.

People behind could not tell why people ahead had stopped.

Which made them step sideways.

Sideways made the door narrower.

The door becoming narrower made people push forward.

Crowds were stupid geometry with opinions.

First bell rang.

Rinna did not delay it.

Good.

Mustache kept checking names.

Ticket woman kept counting.

I started directing already-cleared late arrivals away from the front table.

"House left wait against wall. Keep center open."

A man said, "We're row three."

"You're still wall."

"We paid for row three."

"And row three will continue existing after scene one."

He did not like me.

Fine.

Nessa appeared beside me.

"How many cleared?"

"Eight left. Four right. More at table."

She looked once.

"Open right rear after cue. I'll take right. You keep left."

Good.

No heroism.

Split load.

The front doors remained open for late intake.

First scene began.

We could hear the second voice carrying Hara's quieter lines into the room.

From the back wall, it worked.

No giant spoon noises.

Jori had won his argument with brass.

Blue lamp under left rail stayed lit.

We waited.

Waiting people became restless in different ways.

Some whispered.

Some stared at me as if I personally controlled theater time.

One child sat on the floor.

Excellent child.

The row-three man asked, "How long?"

"Until the blue lamp goes dark."

"How long is that?"

"Less time if you don't make me invent an answer."

His wife laughed.

He stopped asking.

Then another group arrived.

Five.

The back hold was now too deep.

Exactly the geometry we'd checked.

Fourth layer blocked the front-door line.

I moved the outermost waiting group into a shallow side pocket near the program table.

Not the flower-frame pocket.

Different side.

Kept walkway between.

The young ticket woman saw what I was doing and shifted the empty program basket off the floor without being asked.

Distributed competence.

The center opened again.

Blue lamp went dark.

"Left group," I said. "Follow me. Quiet."

I did not take all of them.

That would be stupid.

First six.

Enough to clear pressure.

I moved down the left aisle.

Crutch tips controlled.

Not silent.

Quiet enough.

The stage was in transition.

Canvas wall turning.

Two actors crossing behind light.

Audience attention forward.

I delivered people to rows.

Row-three man tried to turn the wrong direction.

His wife pulled his sleeve before I did anything.

Marriage as route control.

I returned along wall side.

Second group.

Another six.

Then two.

Nessa handled right rear.

Within minutes the back wall had become wall again instead of human storage.

The front table still had late arrivals, but normal numbers now.

Problem compressed.

Not solved forever.

Just handled.

I liked work with edges.

Act one continued.

For most of it I stood near left rear and did almost nothing.

This was correct.

Aisle work was successful when no one needed aisle work.

My shoulders began to complain anyway.

Standing on crutches for long periods was not walking.

Walking distributed work differently.

Standing made every minute collect in the same places.

I shifted.

Sat briefly on the back ledge during a long scene when Nessa covered the door.

No one praised endurance.

Good workplace.

At interval, everything became movement again.

People stood.

People who had spent an entire act knowing where the exits were suddenly forgot.

Wine remained banned inside.

This caused democracy to fail again.

The woman with the cane came out along left aisle.

No issue.

She stopped near me.

"You work here now?"

"Tonight."

"You were in that portrait."

I stared.

"What?"

"Street studies. Senna."

There it was.

Reputation through art.

Terrible.

"Apparently."

"You looked annoyed in it."

"Accurate artist."

She smiled and kept walking.

That was all.

No destiny.

No patron.

Just somebody had seen my face somewhere I wasn't.

I hated Chapter Whatever of my life.

Second act created the flower-frame problem.

Not because the frame failed.

Because audiences loved standing exactly where work needed to happen.

Two men lingered in the marked rear-left pocket talking through the act-start bell.

I said, "Need this space clear."

One moved immediately.

The other said, "We're not in the aisle."

"Correct. You're in the frame path."

He looked around.

"What frame?"

Perfect timing.

Behind him, Nessa and another worker appeared carrying the tall flower frame sideways.

The man moved.

Evidence-based management.

Nessa did not break stride.

"Thanks," she said.

"Demonstration helped."

Frame vanished backstage.

Act two started.

House settled.

I heard Marek enter.

On time, as far as I could tell.

Growth everywhere.

Then a messenger came to the front door from North Gate.

Not for me.

For the theatre generally.

The mustached worker read the board-copy notice and brought it to Rinna during the next safe interval.

I saw only the header as it passed.

NORTH SECONDARY CLOSED UNTIL MORNING REVIEW

Fine.

No urge to chase it.

A whole road could remain wrong without Greg attendance.

Excellent.

The rest of the show was mostly work.

Not story.

Not magic.

Work.

Late couple after second act start.

Dropped scarf under row six.

Child needed privy during quiet scene.

One chair shifted into aisle because a large man leaned on it while standing.

A program crumpled into something wet for reasons I refused to investigate.

At bows, audience became a crowd in reverse.

This was almost worse.

Everyone knew the exit.

Everyone wanted it simultaneously.

Nessa opened both rear paths.

I kept left traffic moving and stopped people from clustering just outside the door to discuss the thing they had literally just seen.

"Keep moving past the threshold."

"We're waiting for someone."

"Wait six steps farther."

"Why?"

"Because everyone behind you also owns a body."

They moved.

Useful principle.

The woman with the cane left without needing me.

Row-three man left complaining about Marek's character.

His wife appeared delighted.

The independently operating child had acquired a program from somewhere despite his family already holding four.

Normal theatre magic.

House clear took longer than performance ending.

Not much.

Enough.

Then reset.

Gray sash off.

Programs collected.

Two chairs returned to line.

One rope post moved back to marked position.

A dropped glove to lost-property box.

Crushed paper.

Cup outside door.

Not inside.

Civilization held.

Nessa and I worked house left while the ticket woman counted something at front table.

Mustache locked one cash box.

Rinna walked the rows once.

"Clear?"

Nessa said, "Left clear."

I said, "Back clear."

Right-side worker called clear.

Rinna nodded.

No ceremony.

The ticket woman handed Nessa a small folded pay slip and two copper.

Nessa handed both to me.

"Count."

I did.

Two.

"Sign."

I signed the slip.

There.

Work converted to money.

No mystery.

I now had three copper.

It felt good.

Not miraculous.

Good.

Rinna passed behind us.

"Third night still good?"

"Yes."

"Same call."

"Unless North Road eats the city."

She looked at me.

"Did it eat anything?"

"Mostly distance."

"Then it can wait until morning."

Correct.

I walked home tired.

Not destroyed.

Shoulders heavy.

Hands fine.

Right leg fine.

Left knee fine.

The oval clicked at the theatre door.

Normal.

At the building door.

Normal.

At the upstairs doorway.

Normal.

I put three copper on the table upstairs.

One old.

Two new.

Not all my money in the world anymore.

Still all my liquid money.

Different feeling.

I looked at the holdfast shelf.

Mara's simple controlled observation cost two copper if nothing special.

I could now pay it and keep one.

That was new.

I did not immediately decide to.

Also new.

The rest stone sat boxed below it.

One copper purchase.

Known fault.

Still unopened.

Oval in my bag.

Long mile note on scrap.

Everything wanted attention if I let it.

I sat instead.

Lyssa came up a little later.

"Paid?"

I showed her the copper.

"Two?"

"Two."

"How bad?"

"People are badly designed."

"Theatre?"

"People."

She nodded as if this confirmed prior research.

"North road?"

"Closed until morning review."

"You going back?"

"No."

She looked at me.

Not surprised.

Just checking.

That mattered too.

"Third night?"

"Still on."

"Then sleep."

"That's authoritarian."

"Yes."

I slept.

Before sleep, though, I had one thought I almost wrote down.

The long mile had reminded me the world could stretch a road without asking.

The theatre had reminded me twenty people arriving late could turn a doorway into a problem without any magic at all.

Geometry was apparently hostile in multiple disciplines.

I did not write that either.

Too broad.

Also I was tired.

# CHAPTER 343

## THE DUE DATE

Vale sent me a bill the morning after I earned money.

This felt supernatural.

The paper arrived folded once, clean, and offensively ordinary.

Cala took it from the boy at the door, read the outside, and handed it to me without comment.

That was worse than commentary.

The front said:

GREG

VALE OFFICE

I opened it.

The inside said:

ACCOUNT REVIEW

THIRD DAY / AFTER MIDDAY

BRING RECEIPTS

NO PAYMENT REQUIRED AT REVIEW

I read the last line twice.

Lyssa looked over from the table.

"Bad?"

"Not immediately."

"That's not an answer."

"It's Vale. That's as close as they get."

Cala put bread down.

"What does it say?"

I handed her the paper.

She read it.

"Bring receipts."

"I noticed."

"Do you have them?"

"Some."

That got both women looking at me.

"I have the Vale one."

"Some," Lyssa repeated.

"The important some."

"Greg."

"Yes."

"Where?"

"Stored."

"Where?"

"Safely."

"Where?"

I went upstairs.

This was not retreat.

It was document retrieval.

The Vale receipt from the seated office hour was where I had left it.

Good.

The expired Antonius cash-work note was there too.

Not relevant to Vale debt unless they wanted evidence I had once missed money, which seemed unnecessarily intimate.

I brought down the Vale receipt.

Lyssa checked it.

"One copper credit."

"Yes."

"Any others?"

"Not from Vale."

Cala asked, "Do you know what you owe?"

I paused.

"Approximately."

Silence.

"That is a bad word around debt," she said.

"I know what the original arrangement was."

"Do you know the current number?"

"No."

There.

Clean.

Ugly.

True.

The exact remaining Vale balance had become one of those facts I had carefully not needed while surviving other things.

This was acceptable when poor enough that exact arithmetic changed nothing.

I was less poor now.

Not rich.

Three copper was not rich.

Three copper was enough to make ignorance embarrassing.

I folded the review notice around the receipt.

"Third day."

Lyssa said, "Same day as theatre?"

I counted.

Yes.

Third night was second theatre shift.

Vale after midday.

Theatre second bell.

Not necessarily conflict.

"Probably both."

"Probably?"

"Vale first. Theatre later."

"And if Vale takes longer?"

"No payment required at review. It should be review."

She gave me a look.

"You said approximately about your debt."

"I am being punished enough."

Cala said, "Eat."

I ate.

The review was two days away.

Which meant I had two days to become a person who knew what he owed.

Unfortunately, the person who could tell me was Vale.

I considered going early.

Then didn't.

The notice gave a time.

I had other things to do.

This was another new behavior I disliked noticing.

I did not sprint toward uncertainty simply because it had sent stationery.

I spent the morning doing almost nothing important.

Laundry.

A market errand.

Rewrapping the charcoal because it had escaped its paper.

Tavi asked whether debt could be tied to a chair like his spoon carrier.

I asked why he knew we were talking about debt.

"You said debt."

Fair.

"No."

"Why?"

"Because debt is imaginary until somebody with paper finds you."

Cala said from the other room, "Don't teach him that."

"Debt is a serious financial obligation recorded between parties."

Tavi looked disappointed.

Good.

Education should occasionally disappoint.

By afternoon my shoulders had stopped complaining about theatre.

I had three copper.

Mara's simple controlled holdfast observation cost two if nothing special was required.

The thought returned.

Not urgently.

Just available.

I could go.

Pay two.

Keep one.

Learn something.

Then Vale's folded notice sat on the table.

I looked at it.

The two expenses were not actually competing yet.

Vale explicitly said no payment required at review.

But information could change decisions.

That was the whole point of information.

Spending two copper before learning my debt schedule would be technically affordable and strategically stupid.

I hated maturity.

The holdfast stayed on the shelf.

The rest stone stayed boxed.

I went for a walk instead.

Free activity.

Mostly.

Carrow had posted a North Gate board by then.

NORTH SECONDARY CLOSED

DAY TRAVEL VIA EAST CUT / LOWER ROAD

REVIEW PENDING

No explanation.

Good public notice.

You did not need magical theory to choose another road.

Two men nearby were providing magical theory anyway.

"Old road spirit," one said.

"There isn't a road spirit."

"Then why's the road doing it?"

"Roads do things."

"That's a road spirit."

I kept walking.

Democracy had reached infrastructure.

At Tool Lane I passed the old no-sign stall where I had bought the repair basket.

The man was there.

Different baskets.

Different junk.

He recognized me.

Not warmly.

Not coldly.

Recognition.

"Anything in that lot work?" he asked.

I stopped.

Dangerous question.

"Some of it held a spoon."

He stared.

"What?"

"Household engineering."

"You bought repair sweep."

"And received repair sweep. Excellent transaction."

He grunted.

I did not tell him about the oval.

That was not dishonesty.

That was ownership.

His current nearest basket had bent brass clips, two cracked wooden handles, a little iron wheel, and something that looked like half a lamp throat.

Price card: 1 C.

I had three.

I walked away.

This was getting ridiculous.

I could afford things and not buy them.

Somebody should have warned me money contained this much restraint.

The second day passed.

No message from theatre.

No message from Hessa.

No message from Beren.

No road emergency.

No artifact screamed.

Tavi's spoon carrier lost another loop.

Normal life survived.

On the third day, I put the Vale receipt in my shoulder bag.

I also put the review notice in.

Then removed the oval.

No reason to bring it.

That felt good.

Vale's office looked exactly like a place designed to make numbers feel physical.

Same narrow front room.

Same desk.

Same shelves.

Different clerk.

The woman behind the desk had short gray hair and a small ink stain on the side of her thumb.

"Greg?"

"Unfortunately."

She did not care.

Excellent.

"Receipt."

I handed it over.

She checked the paper against a ledger.

Not one ledger.

Two.

One thick bound book.

One narrower account book.

She found my line in the second.

"One-copper service credit posted."

"Good."

"You were told review today?"

I showed notice.

She checked that too.

"Sit."

I sat.

Crutches against chair where I could reach them.

She turned the account book toward herself, not me.

Healthy boundary.

Then she read.

"Original balance recorded at thirty-two copper equivalent."

There it was.

A number.

Not new history.

Just finally exact in current accounting.

I kept my face still.

Thirty-two.

"Credits posted since opening: six copper equivalent total."

I looked at her.

"Including the one?"

"Including the one."

"So twenty-six remaining?"

"Principal equivalent, yes."

Principal.

Ugly word.

"And not principal?"

She looked at the book.

"No penalty balance currently posted."

Currently.

"Interest?"

"Not under this account structure while review terms remain current."

That was much better than expected.

"What makes review terms not current?"

She tapped the notice.

"Missing required review without arrangement. Refusing account verification. Default event under original agreement."

"What counts as default event?"

"I can read the listed categories."

"Please."

She did.

Death.

Flight from district without notice.

Fraudulent identity.

Refusal of contracted service credit when properly scheduled under agreement terms.

Failure to attend two consecutive required reviews without arrangement.

A few others that did not apply to me.

I listened carefully.

"Am I currently in default?"

"No."

"Any penalty pending?"

"No."

"Any payment due today?"

"No."

"Any payment due before next review?"

"No fixed cash payment."

There.

I breathed normally.

"Then what is this review for?"

She finally looked directly at me.

"To make sure both sides agree the account exists."

That was almost funny.

"I agree."

"Good."

"Do you?"

"The ledger does."

More romantic than Vale deserved.

She continued.

"Current account remains service-credit eligible. Cash payment accepted but not required under current terms. You may request service opportunities when available. Vale may also offer qualifying service. You may refuse individual opportunities unless the original agreement marks them mandatory."

Important.

"How do I know which are mandatory?"

"Written offer states it."

"Have I refused any mandatory service?"

"No."

Good.

"Can I get the remaining balance in writing?"

She paused.

"Account copy is one bit per page."

Of course.

"How many pages?"

"Your current summary is one. Original agreement is four."

I had zero bits.

No conversion.

I could not simply decide a bit was some fraction of copper and make arithmetic happen.

"Can you write the number on the review receipt?"

She considered.

"I can stamp current reviewed principal equivalent."

"Free?"

"Included in review."

Civilization survived again.

She took a small square slip.

Stamped date.

Stamped REVIEWED.

Wrote:

PRINCIPAL EQUIVALENT REMAINING: 26 C

NO PENALTY BALANCE POSTED

NO FIXED CASH PAYMENT DUE BEFORE NEXT REVIEW

Then added a mark and initial.

I read it twice.

"Next review?"

"Sixth week from today unless notice changes it."

"Can notice change it earlier?"

"For qualifying account event."

"Meaning?"

"Service scheduling. Dispute. Payment posting error. Default concern. Account transfer."

"Account transfer?"

"Vale can transfer servicing rights under original agreement."

That was less pleasant.

"To anyone?"

"No."

"Who?"

"Eligible registered account holder. Terms remain attached unless agreement permits amendment."

"Does mine?"

She looked at the ledger.

"Not without your signature for payment structure. Servicing holder can change."

Interesting.

Not immediately threatening.

Worth knowing.

"Any transfer pending?"

"No."

Good.

I asked one more question.

"If I pay cash, does one copper reduce one copper equivalent?"

She looked at me like this was finally a sensible question.

"Under current account, yes, for copper payment."

"Bits?"

"Converted at posted office rate on payment day."

There.

A conversion existed institutionally.

Not established as universal fixed currency conversion.

Good.

"What's today's rate?"

"Are you paying bits?"

"No."

"Then it doesn't matter."

I smiled.

I liked her too.

"Fair."

She stamped my old one-copper receipt as VERIFIED and handed both papers back.

"Sign review line."

I signed.

"Done?"

"Done."

"That's all?"

"Were you hoping for debt theater?"

I stared at her.

"Do you know where I work tonight?"

She looked confused.

Good.

Coincidence remained alive.

"Never mind."

I stood.

Three copper still in my purse.

Twenty-six copper equivalent still owed.

No payment required today.

No penalty.

No hidden crisis.

The debt was simultaneously better and worse now that it had a number.

Twenty-six was not impossible.

That was the problem.

Thirty-two had once belonged to the category of enormous bad thing.

Twenty-six now belonged suspiciously close to the category of expensive artifact opening bid.

I had watched a harness open at twenty-six.

I had watched it sell for thirty-six.

I had three.

Numbers were beginning to occupy the same world.

That changed them.

Outside Vale, I sat on a low public wall for a minute.

Not because I was devastated.

Because my shoulders preferred sitting when thinking.

Twenty-six debt.

Three cash.

Two more possible tonight if theatre happened as scheduled.

Not earned yet.

Mara holdfast observation two.

Rest stone one already spent.

Future artifacts twenty, thirty, forty, more.

I could pay Vale one copper now.

I had not.

Should I?

Maybe.

But the account charged no current penalty and required no fixed cash payment.

Cash had option value.

Debt had psychological ugliness.

Those were different things.

I wrote on the narrow scrap:

VALE REVIEW

26 C PRINCIPAL EQUIV REMAINING

NO PENALTY CURRENT

NO FIXED CASH DUE BEFORE NEXT REVIEW

SERVICE CREDIT ELIGIBLE

NEXT REVIEW 6 WEEKS UNLESS NOTICE

Then:

CASH PAYMENT 1 C -> 1 C EQUIV TODAY

I almost wrote PAY IT FAST.

Stopped.

That was emotion pretending to be strategy.

Then I almost wrote DON'T PAY CASH.

Stopped again.

That was strategy pretending certainty.

I wrote:

DECIDE WITH OPPORTUNITY COST.

Ugly.

Useful.

I went home before theatre.

Lyssa was there.

"Well?"

I handed her the stamped summary.

She read it.

"Twenty-six."

"Yes."

"You knew approximately?"

"My approximation was emotionally correct."

"What does that mean?"

"Large."

She read the rest.

"No payment due."

"Correct."

"No penalty."

"Correct."

"So you're okay."

"Currently."

She handed it back.

"Are you paying some?"

"Not today."

No argument.

No lecture.

She just nodded.

Good household.

Cala asked the more dangerous question.

"Are you still going to theatre?"

I checked the time.

"Yes."

"Then eat first."

Of course.

Debt became dinner.

Dinner became work.

Work might become two copper.

Two copper might become reserve.

Reserve might become an artifact.

An artifact might become a problem.

Life had developed a suspiciously functional loop.

I did not write that down either.

# CHAPTER 344

## THE QUIET GUEST

The second theatre shift began with a woman whose face I could not remember.

This was not immediately concerning.

I forgot faces all the time.

Most people did not deserve permanent storage.

She arrived before first bell wearing a dark green coat, pale gloves, and a silver clasp at her throat shaped like a closed eye.

I remembered all of that.

I remembered her ticket.

House right.

Row five.

Near aisle.

I remembered the young ticket woman checking the paper twice.

I remembered the woman saying, "I was told active privacy pieces were permitted if declared."

I remembered Rinna being called.

I remembered the silver clasp.

I did not remember the woman's face.

Not badly.

Not vaguely.

I could look directly at her, turn away, and lose the arrangement.

Hair color stayed.

Height stayed.

Age category approximately stayed.

Face did not.

I hated it.

Rinna did not.

Or hid it better.

She stopped three steps away from the woman.

"Declared where?"

The woman produced a small folded registration slip.

Rinna read it.

"This says personal privacy field, low reach, no identity substitution."

"Correct."

"Active now?"

"Yes."

"You need to deactivate during admission check."

The woman stiffened.

"Why?"

"Because my staff must be able to match you to your ticket if you leave and return."

"The registration is valid."

"I'm not disputing the registration. I'm telling you the house condition."

Good.

Not artifact law.

Theatre rule.

The woman looked toward the front table.

"I was told active privacy pieces were permitted."

Rinna said, "Some are. This one prevents reliable visual recognition at the door. Deactivate for admission. You may reactivate once seated if its field stays inside your row and does not interfere with performance equipment or other patrons."

That was more specific than I expected.

The woman touched the silver clasp.

A tiny blue line appeared beneath her thumb.

Then vanished.

Her face arrived.

Not magically dramatic.

Just available.

Long nose.

Dark brows.

Small scar near left side of mouth.

Maybe thirty-five.

Maybe forty.

The ticket woman checked her against the ticket mark and registration slip.

Then nodded.

The woman reactivated the clasp after crossing into the house.

Her face became disposable again.

I leaned toward Nessa.

"I hate that."

"Good. Don't buy one."

"How expensive?"

"No idea."

Healthy.

"Does theatre get many?"

"Privacy pieces? Sometimes. That kind? Not often."

"Why allow it active inside?"

"People pay to watch a play, not be watched by strangers."

Fair.

"And if it affects the stage?"

"Then we tell them to turn it off."

Also fair.

The woman disappeared into row five without disappearing at all.

My job tonight was almost the same on paper.

House route.

Late reset.

Two copper after clear.

But the North Secondary closure had reduced the late-arrival problem instead of worsening it.

People had adapted.

Carrow was irritatingly competent when given a full day.

Traffic came through East Cut earlier.

Patrons arrived in smaller groups.

The front table did not become a human dam.

Good.

I had prepared for the wrong problem.

Normal work.

Rinna changed nothing because there was nothing to change.

I checked house-left.

Blue lamp.

Back wall.

Flower-frame pocket.

Rope post.

All fine.

Nessa checked right.

The young ticket woman counted.

Mustached worker said polite no to a man trying to bring a bottle inside.

Civilization repeated itself.

First bell.

Doors narrowed.

Second bell.

House settled.

The show started.

I stood rear-left.

Tonight my shoulders were less stupid.

Not stronger.

I knew when to sit.

Different thing.

The first act ran clean.

Marek entered from the correct door.

Hara carried a quiet line through the second voice.

No giant boots.

No late crowd wave.

I began to suspect I might earn two copper through simple competence.

Then row five started whispering.

Not loud.

Wrong.

A cluster of people around the privacy-clasp woman kept turning toward one another.

I could not hear what they said.

At first that seemed polite.

Then I realized I could not hear anything from that section.

Not whispers.

Not clothing.

Not chairs.

Not the stage.

The audience in a rough circle around row five had become acoustically absent.

Hara spoke.

The second voice carried her line cleanly across the room.

Except near row five, where the line seemed to fall into a hole.

People inside the hole leaned forward.

One man cupped a hand to his ear.

There.

Not privacy of face.

Something else.

I caught Nessa's eye from across rear house.

She had noticed too.

Good.

No lone genius.

She pointed two fingers toward row five, then toward Rinna at back center.

I moved along wall.

Quietly.

Rinna met me halfway.

"Row five."

"I know."

"Sound gone in a radius."

"How far?"

"Maybe two seats either side. Hard to tell."

"Clasp?"

"Probably."

She looked toward the stage.

Scene still running.

No immediate danger.

Performance disruption localized.

Rinna waited for the next scene-change cue.

Then went down the right aisle herself.

I stayed rear-left.

Again, good.

Authority handled patron artifact.

I handled my route.

The blue lamp went dark.

Canvas wall turned.

Rinna reached row five and crouched near the woman.

I could not hear them.

Of course.

The woman touched the clasp.

Blue line.

The acoustic hole vanished.

Audience noise returned all at once.

Chair creak.

Breathing.

A cough.

The stage.

Then the woman's face became available again too.

Interesting.

Same activation controlled both effects?

Maybe.

Or one failure had dragged another function with it.

No conclusion.

Rinna spoke to her.

The woman looked angry.

Not embarrassed.

Important distinction socially.

She produced the registration slip again.

Rinna looked at it.

Then shook her head once.

The woman stood.

Row five had to let her out.

I moved down left? No.

House right.

Not mine.

Nessa handled aisle.

I stayed where I was.

This restraint deserved money.

The woman was escorted to rear house, not thrown out.

Rinna brought her to the side vestibule near front table.

The show continued.

Most audience watched the stage.

A few watched the vestibule.

Humans preferred nearby administrative conflict to fictional tragedy.

At the next safe interval, Nessa came to me.

"Keep left."

"Already doing that."

"Good."

"What happened?"

"Piece widened."

"Privacy field?"

"Apparently."

"Sound suppression listed?"

"No idea yet."

She left.

Healthy information boundary.

I loved competence when it denied me gossip.

The act continued.

Without the clasp active, row five heard normally.

The woman's seat remained empty.

At interval she was still in vestibule.

Now her face stayed visible.

The silver clasp was inside a little folded cloth pouch on the table.

Not confiscated exactly.

Contained.

Rinna stood with the woman and the mustached front worker.

A city house-ward runner had arrived.

I knew the type by sash.

Not police.

Not artifact examiner.

Public building safety runner.

At least broadly.

He had a narrow wooden case and a copper-tag board.

I did not approach.

Then Rinna called me over.

So I approached.

"Greg."

"Yes."

"You were rear-left when it changed?"

"Yes."

The runner looked at me.

"Describe only what you observed."

I liked him immediately.

"Before act one, active clasp made her face difficult to retain. Registration was shown. Rinna required deactivation during admission. Face became normally memorable. She reactivated after seating."

"Then?"

"During act one, roughly row-five area became quiet in a way that removed stage sound too. I couldn't hear whispers, chair noise, or performance sound from a small area around her. I did not measure radius."

"Did you see activation at that moment?"

"No."

"Did you see her touch piece before effect began?"

"No."

"Did you see effect end?"

"Yes. Rinna reached her during scene change. Woman touched clasp. Blue line showed briefly. Sound returned. Her face also became easy to retain again."

"Any flash, heat, smoke, smell, physical injury?"

"None I observed."

"Anyone collapse?"

"No."

"Anyone unable to leave?"

"No."

"Good."

He wrote.

The woman said, "It has never done that."

Runner said, "I'm not deciding why it did."

Excellent.

She pointed at registration slip.

"It's licensed for privacy use."

"Registration describes declared function at registration. It does not certify current condition tonight."

There.

Artifact life again.

Documents described history.

Objects continued aging afterward.

The woman looked toward the pouch.

"Can I take it home?"

"Probably."

She did not like probably.

Neither did I.

Runner continued.

"House can refuse reactivation on premises. I can tag observed incident and advise inspection. If I find active public danger I can hold it under building-safety authority. I haven't established that."

Rinna said, "It stays inactive here or it leaves with her."

The woman asked, "If I leave, do I get readmission?"

Ticket woman answered from the table.

"At interval, yes, if ticket remains matched and house has not closed reentry."

Institutional chorus.

The woman exhaled.

"Fine. It stays off."

Runner opened his narrow case.

Inside were paper tags, thin lead seals, two chalk sticks, a small brass fork, and nothing dramatic.

He did not touch the clasp.

He wrote an incident tag.

Asked the woman to read it.

She read.

"Observed widened effect?"

"That's what witnesses describe."

"Not failure?"

"I didn't inspect it."

Good.

"Not illegal use?"

"Not established."

Very good.

"Not sound suppression?"

"Observed local sound loss. Cause unestablished."

The runner might have been the most attractive bureaucracy I had ever seen.

He attached the paper tag to the cloth pouch string, not the artifact.

"Take it for inspection before next use in public building. House may set stricter conditions."

Rinna said, "We do."

The woman looked at her.

"No active privacy pieces?"

"No. This piece. Until you bring inspection."

Specific.

Again.

The woman nodded once.

Angry but reasonable enough.

The runner left before interval ended.

No arrest.

No confiscation.

No secret assassin.

No cursed artifact revelation.

Just an expensive-looking personal magical object behaving outside expected bounds in a crowded building.

Which was honestly more unsettling.

The woman returned to row five with the clasp inactive in its pouch.

Her face remained visible.

I found myself remembering it aggressively.

Long nose.

Dark brows.

Scar near mouth.

This was petty.

I allowed it.

Second act started.

Then something else happened.

The woman laughed.

Not at the play.

At Marek missing a cup.

He reached for a cup that was not there, looked at his hand, then at Hara.

Hara handed him her cup without breaking line.

Audience laughed.

The woman laughed too.

Normal sound.

Normal face.

Normal person with a malfunctioning object in a pouch.

That mattered.

Artifacts wanted to turn owners into extensions of themselves in my head.

Carrier harness man.

Privacy-clasp woman.

Rest-stone buyer.

As if owning strange magic made a person primarily a magical event.

It didn't.

Usually.

Marek drank from Hara's cup and made a face because it was apparently empty.

She said, perfectly in character, "Even theft requires preparation."

The audience liked that too.

I did not know if it was written.

Better not knowing.

The rest of the shift became ordinary.

Which was almost disappointing.

One late couple.

One child asleep across two seats.

One man standing in the flower-frame pocket again, different man, same species.

At bows the audience exited.

The privacy woman stopped at front table.

She asked Rinna for a written house incident copy.

Rinna gave her one.

Good.

Then she looked at me.

I remembered her face.

Victory.

"You were the witness?"

"One of them."

"Did it bother you before it widened?"

Dangerous question.

"Personally? Yes. Operationally? Not after admission check."

She almost smiled.

"Most people like not being remembered."

"I prefer choosing what I forget."

"Fair."

She touched the pouch at her throat.

"It was my mother's."

There.

A whole history trying to happen.

I did not ask.

She waited half a breath.

Then said, "Good night."

"Good night."

She left.

Nessa appeared beside me.

"You didn't ask."

"Ask what?"

"Her mother."

"I am growing."

"Terrible."

"Painful."

Reset took longer tonight because incident paperwork had stolen Rinna and front-table time.

So I did more chairs.

Programs.

Rope post.

Checked left rear.

Helped Nessa carry the empty program box to storage.

Not backstage.

Storage off front corridor.

Scope survived.

At final clear, Rinna checked the house.

Front doors closed.

Mustached worker counted slips.

Young ticket woman balanced something in her book.

Nessa gave me the gray sash back.

Rinna handed me two copper.

"Second evening."

I counted once.

Two.

"Do I sign?"

"Yes."

I signed.

Five copper total now.

That number felt much larger than three and much smaller than twenty-six.

Economics was rude.

Rinna said, "No more house nights scheduled."

"Good."

She raised an eyebrow.

"I mean, healthy boundaries."

"If we need you, we'll ask."

"Also good."

No standing employment.

No emotional misunderstanding.

A completed bounded job.

I liked that more than I expected.

Nessa asked, "You going to spend it?"

"Why does everyone ask me that?"

"Because you look like you already spent it in your head."

I hated observable behavior.

"I have options."

"That sounds expensive."

"It is."

Outside, the street was cool and mostly dry.

I had five copper.

Twenty-six debt.

A two-copper holdfast observation available.

A one-copper retired rest stone already sitting uselessly at home.

A one-copper oval that had become useful enough to carry and weird enough to distrust.

And now a stranger's inherited privacy clasp had widened into an undocumented sound hole in the middle of a play.

I did not know its price.

I did not know its maker.

I did not know whether the sound loss was a defect, hidden function, aging interaction, bad repair, environmental interference, or something else.

I knew one thing.

Registration had not made it simple.

That mattered more than price.

At home, Lyssa was awake.

"Paid?"

I put two copper on the table beside the other three.

"Five."

She looked at the coins.

Then at me.

"Now what?"

I looked upstairs.

Not literally at the holdfast.

Directionally.

She noticed.

Of course.

"Mara?"

"Maybe."

"Debt?"

"Maybe."

"Keep it?"

"Maybe."

"You're unbearable with money now."

"This is financial sophistication."

"It's indecision."

"Sophisticated indecision."

She laughed.

Then I told her about the privacy clasp.

Her first question was not what it did.

"Did anyone get hurt?"

"No."

"Then what happened?"

I explained.

Face hard to remember.

Declared privacy field.

Later local sound vanished too.

Artifact turned off.

Runner documented observation without pretending to know cause.

Owner kept it, inactive, with advice to inspect before public use.

Lyssa frowned.

"Would you buy one?"

"Absolutely not."

"Because it broke?"

"Because people forgetting my face sounds useful until I remember I enjoy being recognized when somebody owes me money."

"You don't have anyone owing you money."

"Exactly. I need to preserve future capability."

She shook her head.

I carried the five copper upstairs.

Not loose.

Wrapped.

Separate from papers.

The holdfast sat on the high shelf.

The rest stone below it.

Oval wrapped near my bag.

I had enough money now to pay Mara two copper and still have three.

That was different.

At three, the observation had been possible.

At five, it might be comfortable.

Not cheap.

Not automatic.

Comfortable enough that I could ask whether the information was worth two rather than whether losing two would make me stupidly exposed.

That was the economic change.

I did not take the holdfast down.

Not tonight.

I wrote one line on the scrap instead:

5 C CASH / 26 C VALE

Then below it:

PRIVACY CLASP: DECLARED FUNCTION != CURRENT BEHAVIOR

I stared at that.

Too broad.

Of course declared function did not equal every current behavior forever.

I crossed it out.

Wrote:

PUBLIC REGISTRATION DID NOT CERTIFY CURRENT CONDITION.

Better.

Then I added:

OWNER STILL PERSON.

I stared at that one longer.

It was embarrassingly sentimental.

I crossed it out too.

Some lessons did not need durable storage.

I put the charcoal away.

Five copper stayed wrapped.

All three of my strange objects stayed where they were.

For one night, money was the most active artifact I owned.

# CHAPTER 345

## THE BENCH

I paid Mara two copper because I had finally reached the dangerous financial condition known as having enough money to ask a question properly.

Not that morning.

That would have looked impulsive.

I waited until after breakfast.

Maturity.

The holdfast came down from the shelf wrapped exactly as it had gone up.

Ring sideways.

Base on sailcloth.

Packing slats still protecting the distorted housing.

I checked the wrapping before touching anything else.

Nothing had moved.

Probably.

I had not marked the dust.

I was not becoming that person.

Yet.

Lyssa watched from the doorway.

"Mara?"

"Yes."

"Two copper?"

"If simple."

"And if not simple?"

"I stop being a customer."

"Good."

Cala looked at the wrapped object.

"You're carrying that through the house?"

"Inactive."

"That wasn't my question."

"Yes."

"Don't hit the wall."

Reasonable magical safety procedure.

I put it in the shoulder bag only long enough to discover it did not fit comfortably.

The base caught the opening.

The ring made the shape worse.

I removed it.

Wrapped bundle under one arm while using two crutches was also stupid.

I stared at the object.

The object remained economically inconvenient.

Lyssa said, "Basket."

"I know."

"You looked like you didn't."

We had a shallow household market basket with two handles and enough room to keep the holdfast flat.

I lined it with folded cloth.

Set the wrapped object inside.

Then tied the handles together loosely with ordinary cord so the bundle could not bounce out.

This created a new problem.

Carrying basket plus two crutches.

I looked at Lyssa.

She looked at the basket, then my right crutch.

"Not from the crutch."

"I was considering it."

"It'll swing."

"I need to know how badly."

Cruel.

In the end I hooked the basket handle over the right crutch grip below my hand and hated it immediately.

It swung.

No.

I tried a short cord from basket handles to shoulder bag strap.

Better.

Basket rode against my right hip.

Still awkward.

Manageable for a short walk.

Artifacts changed logistics before activation.

Excellent.

I left with five copper in my head and three in my purse because two had already become conceptually dead.

That was not accounting.

That was self-defense.

Mara was at South Registry Row.

Third table from the corner today.

Different stool.

Same expression.

She looked at me.

Then the basket.

"You packed it better."

"Free advice had consequences."

"Rare."

I set the basket down carefully.

"Simple controlled observation. Two copper if simple."

"That's what I quoted."

"Still true?"

"Depends what you brought."

"Same thing."

"Things change."

Fair after last night.

I unwrapped only enough for her to identify the holdfast.

She looked at the opposing-hook mark.

Rear screw.

Distorted seam.

Then at me.

"No opening?"

"Correct."

"No load certification?"

"Correct."

"No repair?"

"Correct."

"Behavior only?"

"If simple."

"Two copper."

I paid.

She counted once.

Three remained.

Not painful.

Noticeable.

Different.

Mara wrote a narrow work strip.

HOLDFAST / CLOSED HOUSING / CONTROLLED BENCH OBSERVATION / NO LOAD CERT / NO OPENING

Then she added:

OWNER PRESENT

"Why that?"

"So nobody thinks I took custody."

Good.

"Do I sign?"

"At end if you agree with observation record."

Also good.

She stood.

"Bring it."

I looked at basket.

Then crutches.

Mara watched me calculate.

"I'll carry basket."

"You sure?"

"I'm charging you."

Excellent economic model.

She took the basket.

We went through a narrow side door behind the row.

Not into some secret artifact vault.

A work court.

Open roof slats.

Stone floor.

Three benches.

Two occupied.

At one, a man was sorting ceramic disks into felt-lined trays.

At another, a woman had a brass tube clamped between wood jaws while she measured something with a black thread.

Neither looked at me long.

Professional paradise.

Mara took the empty bench.

It had a thick wood top scarred by use and a pale stone inset roughly the size of a dinner plate.

Beside it sat a low iron frame with four adjustable feet.

"Observation bed?" I asked.

"Bench plate."

Of course.

"What does it do?"

"Less than you want."

"Promising."

She set my basket on a side stool.

"It gives me a known surface and tells me if gross force leaves the plate boundary."

"How?"

"Edge line changes."

The pale stone had a thin gray line around its rim.

"Magnitude?"

"No."

"Direction?"

"Sometimes enough to see which edge reacts first. Not measurement."

"Mana?"

"It doesn't tell me source."

Good.

A bounded tool.

I loved it already.

Mara added, "If your piece does something outside what I consider simple, I stop."

"And keep two copper?"

"Yes."

"Cruel."

"Bench time happened."

Fair.

She unwrapped the holdfast herself while I watched.

Ring sideways.

Base flat.

No activation.

The thing looked exactly as disappointing as before.

Old dark metal.

Circular base.

Ring.

Housing distortion.

A piece of equipment with the aesthetic confidence of a door fitting.

Mara placed it at center of pale plate.

"First observation."

Nothing happened.

"Strong start."

"Closed housing at rest. No visible response on plate."

She wrote that.

Then she put four little wooden blocks around the base without touching it.

"Reference only."

"So if it moves."

"Yes."

She waited.

Nothing.

Good.

Then she brought out a long wooden rod with a forked end.

No magic I could see.

"I am going to rotate the ring from outside hand reach."

My attention sharpened.

The seller had turned the ring during demonstration.

I had not since buying it.

"How far?"

"Small."

"Define."

"Until first response or one eighth turn. Whichever comes first."

Good.

"And if it resists?"

"Stop."

"Clicks?"

"Stop and observe."

"Moves the base?"

"Stop."

"Anything else?"

"If I dislike it."

Perfect measurement.

She fitted fork over ring.

Slow pressure.

The ring moved.

Barely.

A dry internal scrape.

Then a click.

Mara stopped.

Immediately.

No drama.

The gray edge line on the bench plate darkened at the near-right edge.

Not all around.

A short section.

I leaned forward.

"Force outside plate?"

"Gross response crossed boundary at that edge."

"Direction?"

"Don't make it cleaner than it is."

Good.

She waited.

The darkened line faded.

The holdfast sat still.

Ring now perhaps a finger-width from original sideways position.

"Any movement?" I asked.

"Base didn't visibly shift against blocks."

She checked each reference block.

No obvious contact change.

"Again?"

"Not yet."

She put the wooden rod down.

Then did something I did not expect.

She placed a plain iron washer on the pale plate.

Not touching holdfast.

About a handspan away.

"Why washer?"

"Known loose object."

"Magical?"

"No."

She waited.

Nothing.

Moved washer to other side.

Nothing.

Closer.

Nothing.

"So not attracting metal."

Mara looked at me.

"So this washer did not visibly move at these positions while the holdfast sat in this state."

I smiled.

"I knew I liked you."

"That's unfortunate."

She moved washer away.

Next came a short length of rope.

Thick.

Old.

She laid one end across the plate, again not touching holdfast.

Nothing.

Then a little wooden peg.

Nothing.

A flat iron strip.

Nothing.

"You're checking material response."

"Crude environmental response."

"Because broad family includes anchoring, rigging, restraint, load control."

"Because those are broad guesses and I don't want one object nearby to become a theory."

Better.

She removed everything.

"Now I return ring."

"To original?"

"As close as observed."

Forked rod.

Slow pressure backward.

The ring moved.

Dry scrape.

No click.

Then stopped at sideways.

Mara removed rod.

Bench line did not darken.

Interesting.

"One-way response?"

"One observed response on one movement."

Right.

I shut up.

Mara wrote.

Then she looked at the holdfast for a while.

"That's simple observation."

I stared.

"That's it?"

"You paid for simple."

"I hate honest businesses."

"Good."

I looked at the work strip.

"What did we learn?"

"Read it at end."

"What do you think?"

"Different service."

"Cruel."

"Correct."

I looked at the holdfast again.

It had responded to a tiny ring turn.

Not visibly moved.

Bench plate registered something leaving boundary at one edge.

No nearby loose washer, rope, peg, or strip moved while it sat afterward.

Returning ring did not reproduce visible plate response.

That was not much.

It was more than yesterday.

"Could the plate response be from internal movement?"

"Yes."

"Could it be from force into bench?"

"Yes."

"Could it be lateral?"

"Possibly."

"Could it be magical?"

"Probably, given object history and seller demonstration, but plate doesn't identify source."

"Could the housing distortion be causing asymmetry?"

"Possible."

"Could the ring position matter?"

"Obviously."

"Could it be dangerous?"

"Everything can be dangerous if you use enough confidence."

"Specific."

"I have not established safe load, safe surface, intended fixture, intended attachment, rated direction, service condition, or release behavior."

There.

A beautiful list of ignorance.

"So yes."

"So don't use it."

"You are destroying my hobbies."

"You need cheaper hobbies."

I thought about theatre.

"Apparently not."

Mara gave me the strip.

It read:

CLOSED HOUSING / VISUAL ONLY BEFORE MOVEMENT

RING MOVED LESS THAN 1/8 TURN TO FIRST INTERNAL CLICK

BENCH EDGE RESPONSE: NEAR-RIGHT SECTION DARKENED / FADED AFTER STOP

NO VISIBLE BASE SHIFT AGAINST REFERENCE BLOCKS

NO VISIBLE MOVEMENT OF LOOSE WASHER / ROPE END / WOOD PEG / IRON STRIP IN OBSERVED POSITIONS AFTER RESPONSE

RING RETURNED APPROX ORIGINAL SIDE POSITION / NO SECOND EDGE RESPONSE OBSERVED

NO LOAD APPLIED

NO HOUSING OPENED

NO FUNCTION / SAFE USE / CONDITION CERTIFIED

I read it twice.

"Accurate?" Mara asked.

"Yes."

"Sign owner line."

I signed.

She signed provider line.

Then stamped it.

Not registration.

Just her work mark.

I folded the strip.

"Question."

"One."

"Free?"

"If I decide."

"What would be the next useful observation if I ever spend more?"

She looked at the holdfast.

Not at me.

"Fixture context."

"Meaning?"

"Something called a holdfast that produces boundary response when ring moves is telling you less on a loose bench than it would mounted the way its family expected."

"Do you know how?"

"No."

"Can you determine how?"

"Maybe. Not for two copper."

There it was.

The next price tier.

"How much?"

"I didn't quote."

"Why?"

"Because I'd need to decide whether I can do it safely and whether I need someone else."

Good.

No invented price.

"Would opening housing help first?"

"Maybe. Might also make the object worse."

"Because distorted seam."

"Because old equipment dislikes curiosity."

I respected that professionally.

She rewrapped the holdfast.

Same orientation.

Ring sideways.

Packing slats.

Basket.

"Anything else?"

I almost asked about the rest stone.

I had it at home.

Retired industrial component.

Known release delay outside yard limit.

This was a registry-row work court.

People here had benches.

Procedures.

Maybe Mara knew counterload systems.

Maybe she didn't.

I had three copper left.

I stopped.

One question had been purchased.

I did not need to turn the visit into every artifact I owned.

"No."

Mara looked faintly surprised.

That was satisfying.

"No?"

"Growth."

"Expensive?"

"Two copper."

She handed me the basket.

This time I knew how to rig it to my shoulder strap without experimenting in public.

Also growth.

Outside, South Registry Row was busy.

A woman argued over a maker mark at one table.

A courier carried three sealed boxes with a red route tag.

A man in blue work sleeves had an object shaped like a fork with seven tines and absolutely no business existing.

I wanted to know everything.

I went home.

This was becoming alarming.

At home, Cala saw the basket.

"Still whole?"

"Yes."

"Wall?"

"Also whole."

"Good."

Lyssa asked, "Worth two?"

That was harder.

I put the holdfast back on the high shelf before answering.

Same place.

Ring sideways.

Wrapped.

Out of Tavi reach.

Then I showed her the observation strip.

She read slowly.

"So what does it do?"

"Unknown."

She looked at me.

"You paid two copper."

"Correct."

"And now you know what it does?"

"No."

"You got robbed."

"I know it produces some kind of boundary response after a small ring movement without visibly moving itself. I know returning the ring didn't reproduce the same response. I know several loose materials nearby didn't visibly react. I know Mara thinks fixture context is more useful than more loose-bench guessing."

Lyssa stared.

"You paid two copper to know less."

"Exactly."

"That's stupid."

"It's excellent."

She handed the strip back.

"Why?"

I thought about the privacy clasp.

Registration said privacy.

Current behavior had included silence.

The long mile looked like a road and behaved like an argument about geometry.

The oval looked like a piece of junk and had become a retreat problem I still did not understand.

Knowing what not to claim was becoming expensive.

"Because yesterday I could make up ten explanations," I said. "Today six of them are worse."

Lyssa considered that.

"Still sounds like you paid two copper to lose ideas."

"Bad ideas are expensive if you keep them."

She made a face.

"That sounded wise."

"Damn it."

Tavi came in.

"What did you buy?"

"Less certainty."

"Can I have it?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Very expensive."

He accepted this more easily than Lyssa.

Children understood markets.

I went upstairs later with three copper.

Three.

Not five.

That mattered.

The holdfast had not become useful.

It had become narrower.

That also mattered.

I took out the narrow scrap.

Wrote:

HOLDFAST BENCH / 2 C

<1/8 RING TURN -> INTERNAL CLICK

BENCH EDGE RESPONSE NEAR-RIGHT

NO VISIBLE BASE SHIFT

RETURN TO SIDE POSITION -> NO SECOND RESPONSE

NO LOAD / NO OPEN / NO FUNCTION

NEXT USEFUL QUESTION MAY REQUIRE FIXTURE CONTEXT

Then:

3 C CASH / 26 C VALE

I looked at the rest-stone box.

Did not touch it.

I looked at the holdfast.

Did not touch it either.

The oval was near my bag.

Three strange objects.

Three copper.

Twenty-six debt.

One answer that had mostly removed answers.

Good.

That was what information was supposed to do before it became knowledge.

# CHAPTER 346

## THE PASSING POINT

Hessa sent no note.

She appeared at breakfast.

This was worse.

Cala opened the door, saw her, and stepped aside with the expression of someone allowing weather indoors.

Hessa looked at me.

"South Walk."

"Good morning."

"It is."

"Any reason you are physically here instead of threatening me with paper?"

"You left one question unfinished."

Lyssa looked up from the table.

"He has several."

"One I care about."

I knew which one.

GREG MOVING + EXTERNAL EFFECT.

Hessa pointed at my bowl.

"Finish eating."

"This is kind."

"This is scheduling."

Close enough.

I ate.

No artifacts.

No oval.

No holdfast.

No rest stone.

Hessa checked my bag before we left anyway.

"You don't trust me."

"Correct."

"Healthy relationship."

"We do not have a relationship."

"Healthier."

South Walk was dry today.

Same long stone arcade.

Same open arches.

No wind worth mentioning.

Hessa had already marked the floor.

Not the old pattern.

A narrow walking lane.

Three chalk lines across it.
