One bead suspended at shoulder height beside the middle line.

Stationary.

"There it is," I said.

"Yes."

"You actually came to answer it."

"No."

I stared.

She pointed at the lane.

"I came to test it."

Right.

Important distinction.

"Body check."

"Right leg fine. Left knee fine. Shoulders fine. Hands fine."

"Sleep?"

"Enough."

She waited.

"Adequate."

"Better."

I hated precise people.

"Any work yesterday?"

"Mara bench observation. Mostly sitting."

"Any magic?"

"No."

"Good."

She pointed to first chalk line.

"Walk through. No mana."

I did.

Both crutches.

Right foot.

Body forward.

The bead sat just outside direct path.

I passed it.

Turned at far mark.

Came back.

Again.

Three passes.

"Where is easiest stable contact?" she asked.

"Both crutches planted. Right-foot contact after that."

"Today?"

"Today."

"Good."

Then six minimal draws while walking.

Not new idea.

Calibration.

First two clean during both-crutch plant.

Third smeared during swing.

Fourth clean enough after right-foot contact.

Fifth broke early during transition.

Sixth held better than fifth but not clean.

No revelation.

Useful baseline.

Minimal total fifty-three.

Then Hessa said, "External effect."

I stopped.

"While walking."

"Yes."

There it was.

"Stationary bead?"

"Yes."

"One grain?"

"Yes."

"Same near range?"

"Yes."

"Anything else you want to say before I make this ugly?"

"Do not correct late."

Of course.

She reset bead in tiny cloth loop.

No guide thread this time.

I noticed.

"Why no lower guide?"

"Target stationary. I want visible response if it moves."

"More swing noise."

"Small effect."

Fair.

She gave me first instruction.

"Do not cast at first pass."

"Why?"

"Because you want to."

Hessa was evil.

I walked.

Bead entered peripheral vision.

Passed.

No magic.

Turn.

Return.

No magic.

Third pass.

Still no magic.

"Good," she said.

"I did nothing."

"Correctly."

This training was hostile to narrative.

"Now attempt one."

I started.

Both crutches plant.

Right foot through.

Bead approaching peripheral field.

I drew.

External effect.

Too early.

Nothing.

"Again."

Attempt two.

I waited longer.

Contact maybe.

Bead twitched after I had already passed it.

Late.

"You threw backward," Hessa said.

"I know."

"No. You know result was late. Different."

Annoying.

Attempt three.

I tried to hold target point rather than bead.

Body moved.

Mana shape felt wrong.

I stopped before pushing.

No effect.

Hessa nodded.

"Counted."

"As failure?"

"As attempt."

Right.

Attempt four.

Stable crutch plant before bead.

I initiated draw.

Then movement carried me through.

The bead shifted.

Tiny.

Toward me.

While I was still moving.

I almost stopped walking.

Hessa said, "Keep route."

I kept route.

Turned.

Came back.

"Clean?"

"No."

Of course.

"Why?"

"Effect landed after your best contact phase. You chased with shoulder."

"But it moved."

"Yes."

I hated yes.

Attempt five.

Nothing.

Attempt six.

No effect.

But the shaping stayed cleaner through body transition.

Hessa noticed.

"Separate."

"I know."

"Say it."

"Better shaping through transition does not mean external effect succeeded."

"Good."

Attempt seven.

I deliberately chose a pass where timing felt bad and did nothing.

Hessa said nothing.

"Does skip count?"

"No attempt if no draw."

Good.

Next pass.

Attempt seven actual.

Tiny movement.

Cleaner.

I was moving past bead.

No late correction.

The effect happened near when bead crossed just behind my forward shoulder line.

Hessa stopped me after turn.

"That one."

"Clean?"

"Clean enough to keep."

I smiled.

"Do not."

Smile gone.

"Why?"

"Because now you will try to repeat it instead of observe it."

She knew me.

Unfortunately.

Attempt eight.

Failed because I tried to repeat it.

Hessa looked at me.

I looked away.

"I hate you."

"Useful."

Attempt nine.

I changed nothing intentionally.

Or tried not to.

No effect.

Attempt ten.

Contact.

Not clean.

Bead moved after my right crutch had already advanced.

Attempt eleven.

No effect.

Attempt twelve.

Clean small movement.

Better than seven.

Still not large.

Still one-grain-ish.

Still stationary target.

But I was moving.

Hessa stopped the session immediately.

"Done."

"That's two clean enough."

"Yes."

"Out of twelve."

"Yes."

"Can we do twelve more?"

"No."

"Six?"

"No."

"Three?"

"You are demonstrating the problem."

Right.

Result greed.

Again.

I sat on the low wall.

Not exhausted.

Concentrated.

Different fatigue.

Hessa untied the bead.

I counted.

External effect one hundred fifteen.

Minimal fifty-three.

Deliberate shaping still fifty-three.

No new shaping attempts as separate counted category today.

"So now I can cast while moving."

Hessa looked at me.

I sighed.

"No."

"Correct."

"I can sometimes produce a small supervised near-range external effect on a stationary target while moving through a familiar walking route."

"Better."

"Two clean-enough out of twelve."

"Yes."

"One contact-ish. One ugly success."

"Your labels are less useful than the route notes."

Of course.

She drew three small marks on the chalk lane.

First before bead.

Second beside bead.

Third after bead.

"Where did clean effects occur?"

I walked the route mentally.

"Not at both-crutch plant. Later."

"Yes."

That surprised me.

"My cleanest shaping baseline is earlier."

"Today."

"Today."

"And external contact?"

"Later in motion."

"Possibly."

"Why?"

"That is your next wrong question."

I frowned.

She tapped chalk beside bead.

"Do not ask why before you know whether it repeats."

Fair.

"Could be timing to target geometry."

"Could."

"Could be body transition changing field relation."

"Could."

"Could be visual timing."

"Could."

"Could be random."

"Yes."

"I hate that one."

"Then keep it."

I hated her professionally.

Hessa changed the task again.

Not more magic.

Walking only.

She had me pass the bead at three different lateral distances.

Close.

Normal.

Farther.

No casting.

Then three speeds.

Slow.

Normal.

Slightly quicker.

No casting.

"Why no test?"

"Because you have one session of moving-caster evidence and want to multiply axes immediately."

"Correct."

"No."

"Cruel."

She pointed at my crutches.

"You also change route geometry when speed changes."

"Yes."

"And lateral distance changes target timing."

"Yes."

"So next session can choose one."

There.

Future question without answering everything.

I looked at chalk marks.

"Distance or speed."

"Or neither."

"You enjoy withholding."

"I enjoy interpretable results."

Same thing from my perspective.

She packed bead.

"Any independent practice?"

"No."

"Any artifact activation?"

"Mara bench observation."

"You activated?"

"Mara used remote rod."

"Any mana use by you?"

"No."

"Good."

I told her the narrow result.

Less-than-one-eighth ring turn.

Internal click.

Bench edge response.

No visible base shift.

No exact function.

Hessa listened.

No excitement.

"Do not combine it with practice."

"I wasn't going to."

She looked at me.

"I wasn't currently going to."

"Better."

We left South Walk.

At the outer arch, I noticed a new public notice board.

Not because it was magical.

Because people were reading it.

NORTH SECONDARY

DAY PASSAGE REOPENED UNDER MARKED ROUTE

NIGHT CLOSURE REMAINS

REVIEW CONTINUES

There.

The long mile had changed without me.

Good.

I read once.

Kept walking.

Hessa said nothing.

"You saw that."

"Yes."

"You are not going to ask if I want to go inspect it."

"No."

"Growth everywhere."

"Don't ruin it."

At home, Lyssa asked how many times I fell.

"Zero."

"How many times did magic work?"

"Depends how insulting you want the definition."

"Normal amount."

"Two clean enough out of twelve moving external attempts."

"Is that good?"

I thought.

"It is real."

Better answer.

Cala asked, "Paid?"

"No."

"Spent?"

"No."

"Good day."

Household economics.

Tavi asked whether I could move spoons while walking now.

"Probably badly."

"Do it."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because Hessa would appear in the wall."

He looked at wall.

Reasonable.

I went upstairs.

Three copper.

Twenty-six Vale.

Holdfast high shelf.

Rest stone boxed.

Oval near bag.

I wrote:

53 MINIMAL

53 DELIBERATE SHAPING

115 EXTERNAL EFFECT

MOVING CASTER + STATIONARY TARGET: 12 SUPERVISED ATTEMPTS

2 CLEAN ENOUGH / SMALL / NEAR RANGE

DO NOT GENERALIZE

Then:

CLEANEST EXTERNAL CONTACT MAY HAVE LANDED LATER IN GAIT THAN CLEANEST SHAPING BASELINE TODAY

I stared.

Too confident.

Changed it.

TODAY: CLEAN EXTERNAL RESULTS OCCURRED LATER IN PASS THAN EASIEST SHAPING PHASE

Better.

Then:

NEXT AXIS: MAYBE DISTANCE OR SPEED / NOT BOTH

I almost added something about progress.

Did not.

The counts were enough.

Two clean effects did not make a skill.

But they made the unanswered question smaller.

That was enough for one day.

# CHAPTER 347

## THE GATE HAND

The North Secondary reopening lasted exactly one morning before somebody found a way to turn it into paid labor.

I respected Carrow.

The notice came through Cala.

Not because she had become my employment office.

Because a woman from two streets over had asked whether the crutch man who could read work tags was home.

I objected to the description.

Cala did not.

"She means you."

"That is a very broad category."

"You are the only crutch man in this house."

Technically fair.

The woman waited outside with a folded yellow strip in one hand and a flour mark on her sleeve.

I had seen her before.

Maybe at the water line.

Maybe buying onions.

Carrow had reached the stage where strangers came preloaded with uncertainty.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

She handed me the strip.

"North Secondary gate needs day hands. My brother took one. They still need two more."

I read.

DAY PASSAGE SUPPORT
NORTH SECONDARY
MARKED ROUTE ONLY
MIDDAY WINDOW
CHECK TAG / CALL LOAD / RECORD CLEAR
NO ROAD ENTRY
TWO COPPER
MEAL INCLUDED
REPORT EAST GATE SHED

I read the important line twice.

NO ROAD ENTRY.

Good.

"What does check tag mean?"

"Don't know."

"Call load?"

"Don't know."

"Record clear?"

"Probably write that it cleared."

Reasonable civilian interpretation.

"Why me?"

She shrugged.

"My brother said they want people who can read fast and don't get clever with carts."

I looked at my crutches.

"I am naturally protected from getting clever with carts."

She looked at them too.

"That was my thought."

Excellent hiring practice.

"When?"

"Before midday bell."

I looked at the house.

Lyssa was at the table with a length of gray cloth across her lap and three pins in her mouth.

She removed the pins.

"Go."

"I haven't decided."

"Two copper."

"Money isn't the only consideration."

Cala said, "Meal."

"Thank you for supporting my strategic process."

Tavi came around the table.

"Can I go?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because you would get clever with carts."

He accepted this immediately.

I read the strip again.

No road entry.

Marked-route support.

Two copper.

No standing arrangement.

The long mile had changed yesterday.

Today somebody needed bodies around the change.

That was how cities worked.

Infrastructure moved one inch and employment leaked out.

"I'll go."

The woman nodded.

"East gate shed. Ask for Saret."

There.

A name.

Dangerous.

I put the yellow strip in my shoulder bag.

Then stopped.

The oval was already in there.

I took it out.

No reason.

The rest stone stayed boxed.

The holdfast stayed high.

Three copper stayed upstairs except one I carried by habit.

I was learning to leave magic home.

This was becoming suspiciously mature.

North Secondary looked different under permission.

The last time I had seen the notice, people stood outside reading about closure.

Now the street feeding the gate had become a temporary yard.

Not a real yard.

A borrowed one.

Three freight carts waited nose to tail along the left wall.

Two handbarrows sat under a striped awning.

A city barrier divided pedestrians from freight.

Yellow route cloth ran from the east gate arch toward the road beyond.

Not painted.

Tied.

Every twelve or fifteen paces, a narrow yellow strip had been fixed to a waist-high iron stake.

The strips moved slightly in the breeze.

Each had a dark mark near the top.

Same shape.

Not letters.

Route mark.

People entering on foot were being checked at one side.

Freight at the other.

Nobody looked excited.

That reassured me.

Excited infrastructure was usually bad infrastructure.

The east gate shed was a narrow stone room attached to the wall.

A man stood outside with a board in one hand.

Broad shoulders.

Bald head.

Brown work vest.

"Saret?"

"Yes."

I gave him the yellow hiring strip.

He read it.

Looked at me.

Looked at my crutches.

Then back at the strip.

"Can you stand twenty minutes, sit ten, repeat?"

"Probably longer, but that sounds better."

"Can you write numbers?"

"Usually."

"Can you shout?"

"Professionally?"

"Across six carts."

"Yes."

"Good."

No inspiring speech.

Perfect.

He pointed inside.

There were three stools.

A narrow desk.

Two water jugs.

Stacks of route tags.

A wall board with four columns.

LOAD
TAG
IN
CLEAR

"You're gate hand three."

There was my title.

Apparently.

"What happened to gate hand two?"

"Outside."

Good.

He gave me a thin wooden board with a clip at the top and a paper sheet.

Twenty-four numbered lines.

"Do not invent anything."

"I like you already."

He did not care.

"Freight comes to yellow line. You check tag number against my board. You call number and lane. Inside road caller answers. When they say receive, you mark IN and send cart. When far caller sends clear number, you mark CLEAR."

"Do I inspect cargo?"

"No."

"Check seals?"

"No."

"Open anything?"

"No."

"Ask driver why they have seventeen barrels of suspicious glowing fluid?"

"If tag matches, no."

"Excellent."

"If tag doesn't match, stop and call me."

"Do I enter route?"

"No."

"Touch route stakes?"

"No."

"Move barrier?"

"No."

"Interpret road marks?"

"No."

"Fix anything magical?"

"No."

I smiled.

"Beautiful."

Saret finally looked at me more carefully.

"You ask too many questions."

"I am trying to discover how little my job contains."

"Your job contains numbers and volume."

Even better.

The first cart came fifteen minutes later.

Two wheels.

Canvas top.

Driver with red scarf.

Tag 118.

I checked Saret's board.

118.

Lane freight.

Window midday one.

"One eighteen, freight lane."

A voice from inside the arch answered.

"Hold."

I did nothing.

This was excellent work.

Then:

"Receive one eighteen."

I marked IN.

"Go."

Driver clicked tongue.

Cart crossed gate.

No drama.

Second cart.

Tag 121.

Third.

Tag 119.

Order did not match numbers.

I survived.

Gate hand two was a woman named Eda who handled pedestrian work on the other side of Saret.

She had a round face and the calm irritation of someone who had already explained the same rule thirty times.

"Yellow cloth stays on your right until second marker."

A man asked, "What if I know the old route?"

"Yellow cloth stays on your right until second marker."

"I've walked North Secondary for twelve years."

"Then you have twelve years of experience following the yellow cloth today."

I liked her.

The road beyond the arch looked normal.

This was the problem.

Stone.

Dirt at edges.

Low drainage ditch.

Trees farther north.

Nothing visibly wrong.

No glowing fracture.

No impossible weather.

No road spirit standing with folded arms.

Just route stakes.

Yellow cloth.

Spacing.

City workers.

And farther down, maybe forty paces, a squat object beside the road with three metal legs.

A dark bowl sat on top.

Every cart passed it.

I noticed because every cart slowed.

Not much.

A little.

The bowl gave one dull amber pulse.

Then the far caller sent clear.

I watched three cycles before asking.

Saret was beside me.

"That bowl part of route check?"

"Yes."

"What does it check?"

"Not your job."

Good.

"Who owns it?"

"Road office."

Also good.

"What happens if it doesn't pulse?"

"Nobody sends another cart."

That was enough.

I shut up.

For almost six minutes.

Then a larger wagon arrived.

Four wheels.

Two draft animals.

Tag 126.

The driver stopped at yellow line.

I checked.

126 existed.

But the lane entry beside it had a second mark.

A little hooked symbol.

I had not seen that on the others.

"Saret."

He came over.

I pointed.

"What does hook mean?"

"Stabilized load."

"Different lane?"

"Same lane. Different spacing."

He took my board.

"Call it with hook."

I did.

"One twenty-six, freight, hook."

Inside caller answered, "Hold."

Longer this time.

The wagon waited.

Canvas side shifted slightly.

Something heavy inside settled with a low wooden knock.

Then another sound.

Not from cargo.

A soft three-note chime under the wagon bed.

I looked.

There.

Bolted beneath the rear half of the wagon was a black metal cradle.

Inside it sat a gray stone cylinder with a shallow groove around its middle.

Not my rest stone.

Not same shape exactly.

But same ugly industrial family.

I knew that immediately.

Old memory did not arrive as a clean answer.

It arrived as annoyance.

Warehouses.

Loading frames.

A cracked yard.

Men arguing about recovery intervals because somebody had tried to save eleven minutes and lost a wall.

Rest units.

Not rest for people.

Rest for systems.

The name had always been terrible.

The cylinder under the wagon gave another faint chime.

A tiny brass tongue beside it moved from left to center.

Saret was watching the road.

Not me.

I said, "That stabilizer uses a rest unit."

He glanced under wagon.

"Yes."

"Current model?"

"No idea."

Healthy.

"That one paired to the wagon frame?"

"Ask transport yard if you care. Not during window."

Excellent.

The inside caller shouted.

"Receive one twenty-six."

Saret held up one finger.

"Wait."

The driver did not move.

The black bowl down the road pulsed amber.

Once.

Then again.

Not normal.

Saret's posture changed.

Not panic.

Attention.

Inside caller said, "Hold receive."

Meaning don't send it.

I did not mark IN.

Good.

The driver leaned down.

"Problem?"

Saret said, "Stay."

The draft animals stood.

The wagon's under-frame cylinder chimed again.

That little brass tongue shifted toward the right.

I had seen that behavior before.

Not this unit.

Not this world exactly.

But the principle.

Load buffering.

Release debt.

Stored mismatch.

A system could look stable because the buffer was eating the difference.

Until the buffer reached its own limit.

I kept my mouth shut.

This was not my apparatus.

Farther down the route, two road workers moved to the black bowl.

Neither touched it immediately.

One crouched.

One looked toward the wagon.

Then toward something outside my angle.

The yellow cloth nearest the road edge fluttered.

No wind here.

Interesting.

Not useful.

The road worker raised a red paddle.

Saret said, "Back one wagon length."

Driver obeyed.

Slowly.

The under-frame cylinder's brass tongue moved back toward center.

One chime.

Then stopped.

The black bowl down-road returned to one amber pulse.

Saret looked at me.

"Clear the line."

I called.

"Freight line holding."

Eda repeated to her pedestrians.

"Pedestrian hold."

Nobody entered.

Two minutes.

Maybe three.

Then inside caller shouted.

"One twenty-six return yard. No passage."

Saret relayed it.

Driver swore once.

Not at us.

At economics.

"Reason?"

"Route response under stabilized load."

"Road or wagon?"

"Not established here."

Good.

The driver looked under wagon.

"Unit's green."

Saret said, "Then transport yard can argue with road office."

Institutional romance again.

The wagon backed out.

I did not mark IN.

Saret wrote something on his own board.

I asked, "Does the failed passage count against driver?"

"No."

"Against tag?"

"Tag gets incident note."

"Can they try again?"

"Not this window."

"Different load?"

"Not my decision."

Good.

The four-wheel wagon rolled away.

The line restarted with a smaller handcart.

Normal amber pulse.

Clear.

Then another.

Normal.

City did not collapse because one load failed.

I liked that.

At first rest I sat on stool.

Eda came in.

"First window?"

"Yes."

"You look disappointed."

"I was promised numbers and volume."

"You got numbers and volume."

"Then the road behaved."

"It does that."

"Rude."

She drank water.

I asked, "How long have you done this?"

"Today?"

"That answers enough."

She snorted.

Saret came in with three wrapped meal packets.

Bread.

Cold sliced meat.

Pickled green thing.

No price because included.

Civilization.

We ate in shifts.

While I ate, I kept thinking about the wagon unit.

Not because it was magical.

Because I owned something in the same broad family.

K-14-6.

Retired industrial component.

Known release delay outside yard limit.

Boxed at home.

Untested.

I had been thinking of it as an object.

Maybe that was already wrong.

A rest unit could be less like a tool and more like a system organ.

Useful only because the rest of the machine knew what to do with what it stored.

A lung was not bad because it failed to become a bird when placed on a table.

This analogy was disgusting.

I kept it.

Saret saw me looking toward the road.

"One twenty-six belongs to road office now."

"I know."

"Then eat."

I bit bread.

Then asked, "Can I ask one general question?"

He sighed.

"One."

"Do stabilized loads have different passage spacing because the road responds to the stabilization equipment, or because those loads are usually heavier?"

Saret stared at me.

"That's two questions."

Cruel.

"Pick whichever answer costs less."

"I don't know."

Excellent.

He continued.

"Hook means spacing. Road office set it. We follow it."

There.

Procedure without theory.

Useful anyway.

I finished eating.

Second window was busier.

Seven freight loads.

Twelve pedestrians.

One old woman who argued that yellow cloth was ugly.

Eda agreed and still made her follow it.

A boy tried to touch a route stake.

His father caught him first.

I respected the father.

One cart tag did not match Saret's board.

I stopped it.

The driver insisted it had been approved.

Saret checked.

Wrong day window.

Driver cursed the clerk who issued it.

Not us.

Good.

Another wagon had a blue bowl hanging from rear axle that leaked cold vapor.

I did not ask.

This was growth.

The black route bowl pulsed amber for each load.

One pulse.

Clear.

One pulse.

Clear.

The magical world had become most magical when everyone treated it like traffic.

Near end of window, another stabilized cart arrived.

Smaller than 126.

Hook mark.

Tag 133.

This time I watched the underside before I watched the road.

Different unit.

Flat black plate with two pale stones locked into side channels.

No chime.

A tiny red thread ran between them.

The cart passed.

Route bowl pulsed once.

Clear.

No problem.

Good.

So not all stabilizers meant trouble.

Also good.

The first failed wagon had taught almost nothing about cause.

But it had taught something about category.

These things lived in systems.

Mounted.

Paired.

Read by other indicators.

Bounded by procedures.

Not sitting on bedroom floors while idiots poked them.

I felt personally attacked by engineering.

At final clear, Saret checked my sheet.

Twenty-three lines used.

One mismatch.

One failed passage not marked IN.

All clear marks matched his board.

He nodded.

"Fine."

Highest praise.

"Do I sign?"

"Yes."

I signed at bottom.

He signed.

Then gave me two copper.

No speech.

No offer of permanent work.

No mysterious promotion.

Two copper for one day-window support.

Meal included.

"Any more tomorrow?" I asked.

"Maybe. Board goes up if needed."

"Should I come ask?"

"No. Check board."

Excellent.

I put copper away.

Five now.

Not wealth.

Not rescue.

Work happened because road reopened and temporary labor existed.

I had been available.

That was enough.

Before leaving, I looked once through the arch.

Yellow cloth.

Stake marks.

Black bowl.

Road.

Nothing dramatic.

"Can I walk the passage as pedestrian now?"

Saret looked at me.

"Window closes in six minutes."

"That was not permission."

"No."

"Good."

I went home by the ordinary city street.

Not north.

At Tool Lane I passed the no-sign stall again.

The one-copper repair basket sat out.

Different junk.

I had five copper.

I could buy it.

I did not.

This was less impressive now.

Five copper had changed the emotional category.

One-copper junk was not impossible.

It was merely junk.

Economic growth occasionally looked like becoming harder to manipulate with baskets.

At home, Tavi met me at the door.

"Did a cart explode?"

"No."

"Did the road explode?"

"No."

"Did you yell?"

"Yes."

He brightened.

"What did you yell?"

"One eighteen freight lane."

His disappointment was enormous.

Cala asked, "Paid?"

"Two."

"Meal?"

"Yes."

"Good."

Lyssa looked at my face.

Not my purse.

"What did you find?"

I hated being known.

"Nothing."

She waited.

"Something."

"Of course."

I put my shoulder bag down.

Then went upstairs.

The rest stone box was where I had left it.

K-14-6.

Retired industrial component.

Release delay outside yard limit.

I did not open it.

That mattered.

I brought the whole closed box downstairs.

Cala saw it.

"No."

"I am not activating it."

"No."

"I am not opening it."

She considered.

"Why is it downstairs?"

"Comparison by existence."

"That sounds fake."

Reasonable.

Lyssa moved her cloth aside.

I put the closed box on far end of table.

Tavi approached.

I pointed.

"No."

He stopped.

Growth in multiple systems.

I told them about the wagon.

Only what happened.

Stabilized-load hook.

Different spacing.

Under-frame rest-family unit.

Route bowl gave abnormal second pulse.

Passage denied.

Backing wagon one length returned indicator toward center.

No cause established.

Another stabilized cart later passed normally with different equipment.

Lyssa looked at the box.

"So yours goes under a wagon?"

"Unknown."

"You just said same kind."

"Same broad family."

"Which means?"

"It means I recognized the category, not the application."

Cala asked, "Does this make it safer?"

"No."

"More useful?"

"Maybe as information."

She looked unimpressed.

Correct response.

I tapped the lid.

"I've been treating this like a weird stone that should eventually do something by itself. Today I saw one doing a job as part of a mounted system."

Lyssa said, "So yours might need the rest of the thing."

"Yes."

"What thing?"

"Unknown."

"Very useful."

"It actually is."

She smiled.

Tiny.

"Because now you know less?"

I pointed at her.

"Mockery stolen from prior lesson."

"Still counts."

I took the box upstairs again.

Closed.

Untested.

Back where it belonged.

Then I counted money.

Five copper.

Twenty-six Vale.

No bits.

Magic unchanged.

Fifty-three.

Fifty-three.

One hundred fifteen.

No practice.

No artifact activation.

I wrote on narrow scrap:

NORTH SECONDARY DAY PASSAGE
MARKED ROUTE / LOAD WINDOWS
STABILIZED LOADS GET DIFFERENT SPACING

Then:

OBSERVED REST-FAMILY UNIT MOUNTED UNDER WAGON
SYSTEM INDICATOR MOVED DURING HELD PASSAGE
WAGON BACKED -> INDICATOR MOVED TOWARD CENTER
ROAD RESPONSE ALSO CHANGED
CAUSE NOT ESTABLISHED

Then:

SECOND STABILIZED CART / DIFFERENT UNIT / PASSED NORMAL

Good.

Then the line I actually cared about:

REST UNIT MAY BE SYSTEM COMPONENT, NOT STANDALONE TOOL

I stared at it.

Too broad.

Changed it.

OBSERVED ONE REST-FAMILY UNIT USED AS MOUNTED SYSTEM COMPONENT

Better.

I added:

DO NOT TEST K-14-6 AT HOME

That rule had not changed.

Then:

5 C CASH
26 C VALE

I almost wrote FIND TRANSPORT YARD.

Stopped.

That would be result greed wearing work clothes.

I almost wrote ASK MARA.

Stopped.

Wrong specialist by assumption.

Then:

IF REST STONE RETURNS, FIND SAFE CONTEXT BEFORE TEST

That was enough.

Outside, somewhere north, carts were probably still waiting for another marked window.

The road had reopened without becoming safe in every way.

A wagon had been refused without becoming a disaster.

I had worked a gate without becoming a road expert.

And the useless stone upstairs had become slightly more interesting by staying inside its box.

Annoying.

Useful.

Probably worth two copper and a sandwich.

# CHAPTER 348

## THE CLEARER

The morning after North Secondary, I woke to a table leg blocking the hallway.

Not metaphorically.

A real table leg.

Dark wood.

Square.

Possibly hostile.

I stopped at the top of the stairs and looked down.

The leg belonged to something folded against the wall below.

Four legs.

Two hinged braces.

A broad top wrapped in old canvas.

Lyssa stood beside it with both hands on her hips.

Cala stood on the other side with the expression she usually reserved for weather entering the house without permission.

Tavi was underneath it.

Of course.

"Why is there a child in the furniture?" I asked.

Tavi looked up.

"I'm checking."

"For what?"

"If I fit."

"Result?"

"Yes."

Useful research.

Lyssa pointed at him.

"Out."

He crawled out.

I came down carefully.

One crutch.

Second crutch.

Right foot.

No magical effect.

Progress remained appropriately unimpressive.

The folded thing was not exactly a table.

Too narrow when folded.

Too many braces.

The canvas wrapped around a top wider than our dining table but thinner.

A pale strip of stone showed along one edge.

I stopped.

"What is it?"

Lyssa smiled.

That was dangerous.

"Mine."

Not the answer I had requested.

Cala said, "Work table."

There.

"You bought a work table?"

"Yes."

"When?"

"Yesterday."

I stared at her.

"While I was shouting numbers at carts?"

"You were busy."

"Apparently everyone was."

Lyssa touched the canvas.

"It came this morning."

"I noticed the invasion."

"Two boys brought it."

"Paid?"

"Yes."

"By you?"

Her eyebrows went up.

Right.

Bad question.

"Your money," I said.

"Correct."

"Your table."

"Also correct."

Cala folded her arms.

"Her table currently lives where people walk."

There was the actual problem.

I looked at hallway.

Then main room.

Then the table again.

"Where is it going?"

Lyssa pointed toward the front window.

Cala said, "No."

Immediately.

Lyssa looked at her.

"That corner."

"That corner has a chair."

"The chair can move."

"To where?"

Lyssa looked around.

So did I.

Our house had reached a mature stage of civilization where every empty patch of floor had acquired a purpose.

Chair.

Basket.

Hooks.

Small shelf.

Water sack.

Tavi.

Tavi was not technically furniture but occupied similar space.

I asked, "How wide when open?"

Lyssa lifted the canvas enough to show the top.

"This wide."

That was not measurement.

It was also large.

The top had two hinged leaves that folded inward.

A narrow central plank stayed fixed between them.

Along one long edge ran the pale stone strip I had noticed.

Not decorative.

Inset.

Three finger-widths wide.

Tiny brass points at both ends.

"Light rail?" I asked.

Lyssa looked pleased that I knew one thing.

"Flat line."

"For hems?"

"For anything I need straight."

"Does it heat?"

"No."

"Cut?"

"No."

"Move thread?"

"No."

"Good."

"It makes a line, Greg."

"Lines have become dangerous around me."

Fair caution.

She pulled a little brass tab at the end.

Nothing happened.

Then looked at Cala.

"Not plugged in."

I looked under the frame.

A braided white cord had been tied around one brace.

At its end sat a flat two-pronged house contact.

Common enough.

Not a mysterious artifact.

Our main room wall had one household feed plate near the window for a lamp and small heating stone.

Cala pointed at it.

"Lamp uses that."

Lyssa said, "Not during day."

"And at night?"

"I don't work with it at night if you need the lamp."

Cala did not answer.

Negotiation.

Good.

I asked, "Why this one?"

Lyssa looked at the table instead of me.

"Because I am tired of moving everything every time we eat."

There.

Simple.

Not simple.

Our dining table had become hers whenever cloth work got large enough.

Which meant pins moved.

Bowls moved.

Tavi moved badly.

Cala moved everything back for meals.

Then Lyssa moved it again.

I had watched that happen without naming it as a problem because it was not my problem.

Apparently it had become expensive enough to buy furniture.

"How much?" I asked.

Lyssa looked at me.

"Mine."

Right.

Again.

"You do not need to tell me."

"Good."

I smiled.

"Was it a good price?"

"Yes."

"That I am allowed to know?"

"Apparently."

Cala pointed toward the front corner.

"Price does not make the chair disappear."

The chair had become politically important.

I went over to it.

Plain wood.

One repaired rung.

Usually where Cala sat when mending or shelling something or simply watching the room.

Not decorative.

Not spare.

I looked at opposite wall.

No.

Too close to stair base.

Near door?

Terrible.

Beside dining table?

Would narrow the route.

My route.

I moved through the main room with two crutches every day.

Furniture geometry had consequences.

Lyssa said, "I can fold the table when I'm not using it."

"How flat?"

"This flat."

She slapped the folded top.

Still thick.

"Against wall?"

"Yes."

"Legs stay out?"

"No. They fold."

"Then why is one attacking the hallway?"

"Because the boys left it there."

Fair.

I walked the room.

Not performing.

Actually walking.

Crutch tips.

Right foot.

Turn near table.

Back toward stairs.

The front-window corner gave enough width if the chair moved.

The chair could go near the side wall if one basket moved.

Basket could go beneath hooks if the little stool moved.

Little stool could go upstairs.

I stopped.

This was how empires formed.

One work table and suddenly three territories required resettlement.

"Chair to side wall," I said.

Cala looked.

"Basket?"

"Under hooks."

"Stool?"

"Upstairs."

"Whose stool?"

I looked at stool.

Mine.

Of course.

A low one I used sometimes to sit while sorting notes near the stair wall.

I did not need it there.

I liked it there.

Different thing.

"Mine," I said.

Lyssa looked at me.

Not smiling.

"You don't have to."

That made it worse.

I looked at the room again.

Chair mattered more.

Her work table mattered more than my convenience stool.

Annoying hierarchy.

"Stool upstairs."

Cala said, "That works."

Decision achieved through sacrifice.

Tavi raised his hand.

"Can I have the stool?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I am sacrificing it nobly, not dying."

He lowered his hand.

We started moving things.

Not the work table.

I was not carrying a broad folded frame while using two crutches.

This did not need experimentation.

Cala and Lyssa shifted it together after clearing the route.

I took the small stool upstairs.

One hand occupied by crutch.

The other also occupied by crutch.

The stool became a problem.

I stared at it.

Tavi appeared beside me.

"I can carry it."

I looked at him.

Small child.

Small stool.

Appropriate scale.

"Both hands."

"I know."

"Do not wear it."

"I wasn't."

He had absolutely considered wearing it.

He carried it upstairs.

I followed.

Delegation.

S-class technique.

Back downstairs, Cala had moved her chair.

Basket beneath hooks.

The new route was narrow at one point but not bad.

I walked it twice.

Lyssa watched.

"Enough?"

"For me."

"For everyone?"

I looked at Tavi.

"He has no route discipline. Nothing is enough for him."

"True."

Cala said, "Door still clears."

It did.

Window still opened.

Dining chairs could pull out.

Good.

Then came the table itself.

Lyssa unwrapped canvas.

The wood underneath had old cuts and needle scratches.

Not pretty.

Good work furniture.

One corner had been patched with a darker triangle of wood.

The pale flat-line rail ran nearly the full long edge.

Stamped into one brass end piece were tiny maker characters.

I did not read them.

Not because I couldn't.

Because this was Lyssa's table.

Growth hurt.

She folded the left leaf down.

Brace locked.

Then right.

The table became broad and flat.

Bigger than dining table by enough to matter.

Cala put one hand on it and pushed.

"Moves."

Lyssa frowned.

One rear foot rocked.

The floor was uneven.

Of course.

Old house.

Old boards.

Nothing level except opinions.

Lyssa crouched.

I looked under.

Each leg ended in a threaded wooden foot with a brass collar.

Adjustable.

"Leveling feet," I said.

"I know."

"I was helping."

"You were naming."

Cruel accuracy.

She turned rear foot half a turn.

Cala pushed again.

Still slight rock.

Another quarter.

Stable.

Then Lyssa plugged the white cord into wall feed.

The flat-line rail stayed dark.

She pressed brass tab.

A thin pale line appeared across the tabletop.

Not bright.

Not beam.

A clean white-blue line exactly parallel to the stone rail.

Tavi gasped.

I also wanted to.

Mine stayed internal.

Lyssa laid gray cloth across table.

The line showed through weave as a faint straight reference.

She shifted cloth.

Pulled one edge.

Pinned it.

The line stayed.

No heat.

No cutting.

No moving thread.

Just geometry made visible.

I loved it.

This was unfortunate.

"That is excellent," I said.

Lyssa looked at me.

"It's a line."

"A reliable line is civilization."

Cala touched table edge.

"How much feed?"

"Less than lamp."

"How do you know?"

"Seller showed the mark."

She pointed under brass tab.

Three tiny dots and one open circle.

Cala recognized it before I did.

"Low draw."

"Yes."

There.

Household magical literacy that did not belong to me.

Good.

Cala nodded.

Not approval exactly.

Acceptance.

"Day use. Lamp gets plate after dark."

Lyssa said, "Fine."

"And fold it if we need floor."

"Fine."

"No pins left on it if Tavi is awake."

"That one was already true."

Tavi said, "I don't eat pins."

Nobody had accused him of eating pins.

Concerning defense.

Lyssa spent the next hour actually using the table.

That mattered more than setup.

Gray cloth became two pieces.

Then three.

She used the light line to square one edge.

Chalked another.

Folded a narrow hem.

Nothing magical happened beyond the line.

Which made the magic better.

It was not a miracle.

It was a tool.

A good one.

I sat at dining table and tried not to watch.

Failed.

"What are you making?"

"Work cover."

"For what?"

"Work."

"Excellent detail."

"Customer wants covers for two instrument cases."

"Musical?"

"Maybe."

"Magical?"

"Maybe."

"Do you care?"

"Only dimensions and closures."

Healthy.

She marked another edge.

I said, "So you bought table because case covers?"

"No."

"Because green vest?"

"No."

"Because mysterious future sewing empire?"

She stopped.

Looked at me.

"Because I'm tired of asking Cala whether she needs the table every time I spread something out."

There.

Again.

I shut up.

Cala was in the kitchen side, close enough to hear.

She said nothing.

That was probably wise.

Lyssa continued.

"I have enough work that moving it all twice a day is stupid. Not enough work for a room. Table is enough."

That was a very good sentence.

Not dramatic.

A threshold.

Enough work for furniture.

Not enough for a room.

I wrote it mentally and hated myself.

"Makes sense," I said.

"I know."

She was enjoying this.

Then somebody knocked.

Not Vale.

Not Hessa.

Already a good day.

Cala opened door.

A woman stood outside holding a long narrow parcel wrapped in brown cloth.

Maybe forty.

Maybe less.

Hard to tell under practical hair.

"Lyssa?"

Lyssa stood.

"Yes."

The woman stepped inside only after Cala moved aside.

Good house.

She looked at work table.

Then at Lyssa.

"You got one."

Lyssa smiled.

"This morning."

"Good."

No introduction to me.

Also good.

The woman unwrapped parcel enough to show a long dark sleeve or tube of fabric with a stiffened mouth.

Not clothing.

Maybe equipment cover.

Along one seam ran little copper-colored knots.

Decorative?

Functional?

Not mine.

The woman said, "This is the other one. Same width as first, but mouth sits wrong."

Lyssa took it.

Looked at seam.

Then laid it on new table.

The pale line crossed underneath.

She aligned the stiff mouth.

"Not same width."

The woman frowned.

"It is."

Lyssa measured with cord.

Once.

Then again.

"Body is. Mouth isn't."

She pointed.

"This side turns in sooner."

The woman leaned.

"Can you fix it?"

"Probably."

"Without touching knots?"

Lyssa looked at copper-colored seam knots.

"Yes. I think."

"Think?"

"I haven't opened it."

Good.

The woman nodded.

"I need both by fourth day."

"I can do this one by third if first fit is still right."

"It is."

"Then third day."

They discussed price quietly enough that I could have heard if I tried.

I did not try.

This was harder than not asking Mara about artifacts.

The woman paid something.

Lyssa put coin away without showing me.

Correct.

Then the woman left.

I waited until door closed.

"You have customers coming here now."

Lyssa froze for half a second.

Not because it was wrong.

Because she was deciding whether I had said something stupid.

"Sometimes."

"Since when?"

"A while."

I looked at Cala.

Cala looked innocent.

Terrible liar.

"A while?"

Lyssa said, "You are not here all day every day."

Right.

Important fact.

The house continued existing while I left it.

Again.

I had learned this theoretically.

Practice remained difficult.

"Do they all come here?"

"No."

"How many?"

"Greg."

"Right."

I stopped.

Her work did not need to become my dataset because I had noticed furniture.

The table had arrived because the work already existed.

Not the other way around.

That was the part I had missed.

I stood.

My shoulder bag hung by usual hook.

Five copper upstairs.

Twenty-six Vale.

Holdfast high.

Rest stone boxed.

Oval home.

All my little systems had become very visible to me lately.

Counts.

Artifacts.

Debt.

Work offers.

Routes.

And Lyssa had quietly crossed a threshold measured in floor space.

Enough work for furniture.

I walked the new room route again.

Past dining table.

Turn by basket.

Chair now side wall.

Lyssa's work table open under front window.

Enough clearance.

When folded, more.

"What?" she asked.

"Checking route."

"Again?"

"The room changed."

"It changed six feet."

"That is a lot when your turning radius includes wood."

She looked at my crutches.

"Okay."

Not apology.

Not pity.

Just recalibration.

She moved one scrap basket two inches farther under table.

"Better?"

I walked.

"Yes."

That was enough.

Cala sat in relocated chair.

She tested it by sitting harder.

Apparently satisfied.

"Window draft?" she asked.

Lyssa looked at table.

"Good for chalk. Bad for loose paper."

"You'll learn."

"Yes."

Tavi crawled under table again.

Lyssa looked down.

"Out."

"I'm checking if I still fit."

"You do."

"How do you know?"

"Because you are under it."

Strong evidence.

He crawled out.

By midday the room looked almost normal.

That was the trick.

New things became normal quickly when they worked.

The chair belonged on side wall now.

The basket belonged under hooks.

My stool belonged upstairs.

Lyssa's table belonged by front window.

A thin pale line crossed gray cloth when she pressed the brass tab.

Then disappeared when she released it.

No explosion.

No appraisal.

No mysterious provenance.

No hidden legendary maker that I recognized from sixty years in the future.

Just a used professional tool bought by somebody who needed it.

I found that more interesting than I expected.

That evening she folded both leaves up.

Braces released.

Legs folded inward.

The table became narrow against wall.

Not invisible.

Still hers.

Cala brought chair slightly forward.

Dinner happened.

No one moved a pile of cloth first.

There.

Benefit.

Lyssa looked at clear dining table.

Then at me.

"Worth it?"

I smiled.

"I don't know what it cost."

"Exactly."

Cruel.

Cala laughed.

Tavi asked, "Can the line cut bread?"

"No," all three of us said.

He looked disappointed.

Good.

After dinner I went upstairs.

The little stool sat near my bed now.

Actually better location.

I sat on it.

This was irritating.

Five copper.

Twenty-six Vale.

Magic fifty-three, fifty-three, one hundred fifteen.

I did not add anything.

Nothing had changed in my numbers.

The house had changed anyway.

I opened my narrow scrap.

Almost wrote:

LYSSA WORK TABLE / FLAT-LINE RAIL

Stopped.

Not mine.

I closed the scrap.

That felt strangely important.

Some things could become part of my life without becoming part of my inventory.

Downstairs, the faint click of Lyssa folding the table brace carried up through the floor.

Then Cala said something I couldn't hear.

Lyssa laughed.

The house continued.

I sat on my relocated stool.

Apparently that was my contribution.

Clear space.

Then get out of the way.

# CHAPTER 349

## THE WALKAWAY

Five copper was enough money to make me dangerous in places where everything cost one.

It was useless in places where anything interesting happened.

I discovered this on South Market's upper row.

I had not gone there for artifacts.

This was important.

I had gone because Cala needed lamp wick and the lower stall was out.

Ordinary errand.

One narrow paper strip.

LAMP WICK / MEDIUM / TWO LENGTHS

No hidden destiny.

No mysterious courier.

No one had whispered that a legendary object waited beneath a tarp.

I was simply walking uphill because household lighting had failed to respect narrative pacing.

South Market upper row was narrower than the lower one and more expensive in a way buildings managed before prices did.

Stone awnings.

Glass fronts on three shops.

Metal signs instead of painted boards.

One place selling writing instruments that looked too clean to touch.

One place selling shoes with actual paired displays instead of a basket of survivors.

Then a covered lane where repair sellers, retired guild stock, estate pieces, and things with paperwork problems lived together under the broad category of somebody else's decisions.

I slowed.

Not much.

Maturity.

A woman behind a table was arguing with a man about a cracked warming plate.

"It still warms."

"One side."

"That is more than no sides."

Good market.

I continued.

The lamp-wick seller was apparently three stalls farther.

I almost made it.

Then I saw the buckle.

Not a jewelry buckle.

Too large.

Palm-wide blackened brass body.

Two thick side loops.

One central jaw shaped like a flattened mouth.

Three pale ceramic teeth sat inside the jaw.

A red release tab protruded from the lower edge.

The whole thing rested on gray felt beside four ordinary rigging clamps and a coil of blue cord.

There was a price card.

18 C

I kept walking.

This was excellent discipline.

Three steps.

Four.

Five.

Then old memory arrived late enough to be irritating.

A roof in winter.

Wet slate.

Someone screaming at a foreman because the anchor had locked properly and the release had not.

A man hanging sideways against a chimney with both boots off the surface.

A different city.

Different life.

Same ugly mouth-shaped jaw.

I stopped.

Damn it.

I turned around slowly.

Not because I wanted it.

Because old memory deserved verification.

This was how lies began.

The seller was an older man with a flat gray cap and a left hand missing the last joint of one finger.

He noticed me return.

Of course he did.

"Rigging?" he asked.

"Sometimes."

Not a lie.

I had existed near ropes.

"Roof work?"

"No."

"Then probably not for you."

Excellent sales technique.

I looked at the four clamps first.

One normal iron jaw clamp.

One brass rope guide.

One little hinge thing with a cracked blue stone.

Then the blackened buckle.

"Load lock?"

His eyes sharpened slightly.

There.

Information leakage.

"Old one."

"Working?"

"Demonstrated this morning."

"On what?"

"Bench rail."

"Loaded?"

"Sand sack."

"How much?"

"Enough for demonstration."

Good seller.

No invented certification.

I looked at price.

"Eighteen?"

"Yes."

"For an old load lock with no rated load card?"

"For an old load lock that works."

Fair.

"Maker?"

He shrugged.

"Mark under body."

He did not pick it up.

Also good.

"May I look?"

"Without activating."

"Obviously."

He looked at me.

"Not obvious to everyone."

I believed him.

I leaned closer.

Under one side loop, partly hidden by wear, was a stamped mark.

Three stacked wedges.

Not famous.

Not later.

Not in any history book I remembered.

But familiar.

Kerrin dock pattern.

Or a copy.

Maybe.

I did not say that.

"How old?"

"Seller said pre-revision."

"Which revision?"

"Didn't say."

"Useful."

"I did not charge for the story."

I liked him.

The buckle body had two belt loops, but not for a normal waist belt.

Too wide.

Harness webbing.

The central jaw looked designed to bite onto a fixed rail, rope ring, or structural tongue.

Not something worn alone.

"What's the failure?" I asked.

He smiled.

There it was.

"You know the family."

Damn.

"Broadly."

"Then you know why eighteen isn't thirty."

Maybe.

I kept my face still.

"Tell me your version."

He laughed once.

"Good."

He pointed at red tab without touching it.

"Locks under load. Holds well. Release gets stubborn if the load remains on the jaw."

Yes.

There it was.

"Stubborn how?"

"Sometimes tab will not travel until load is mostly off."

"Mostly?"

"That is the word I was given."

"Not certified?"

"No."

"Repeatable?"

"I saw it twice this morning."

"Loaded both times?"

"Sand sack. Same rail."

"Released both times?"

"After sack was lifted."

The old memory sharpened.

Not an exact item.

Class.

Pre-revision load lock.

They were hated because the thing did its job too well in the worst possible moment.

A later revision added a secondary release path.

I remembered that much.

Maybe.

Or I remembered one maker adding one.

Memory was not a manual.

"So if someone is hanging from it," I said, "the release may be inaccessible until somebody unloads them."

"That would be a stupid use."

"People specialize."

"Correct."

He tapped the price card.

"For temporary work positioning. Not fall arrest. Not rescue gear. Not life support."

Good.

Very good.

"Paper?"

He reached under table and produced a folded strip.

Not original.

Resale note.

I did not take it.

He held it where I could read.

OLD LOAD LOCK / HARNESS MOUNT
WORKING DEMONSTRATION ONLY
NO LOAD RATING
NO LIFE-SAFETY CERTIFICATION
RELEASE REQUIRES LOAD REDUCTION
SOLD AS USED RIGGING EQUIPMENT

Clean.

"Eighteen," I said again.

"Eighteen."

"Why not twelve?"

"Because at twelve a roofer buys it for spare positioning gear before supper."

"Why not twenty-five?"

"Because at twenty-five a roofer buys a newer one with better release."

There.

Market intelligence from a man who did not need my help.

Good.

I should have left.

Instead I looked at the side loops again.

Harness mount.

Load jaw.

Release only after unloading.

Most users had two feet, two hands, and one problem.

I had one foot, two crutches, and several problems.

Which sometimes rearranged the arithmetic.

If the lock held body position against lateral load, then unloading did not necessarily require another person.

Not for me.

Maybe.

Two crutches could take weight while the right foot came clear.

Or one crutch and a seated transfer.

Or the lock could mount to a belt or work harness while my hands controlled release after body load shifted onto crutches.

That was not proof.

It was not even a plan.

It was a reason the known downside might be less severe for me than for a standard user.

Which was dangerous.

Because eighteen copper was suddenly not a price.

It was a problem.

I had five.

Twenty-six Vale.

No immediate payment due.

No scheduled theatre work.

No standing gate job.

Five liquid copper.

Thirteen short.

I hated arithmetic when it became desire.

The seller watched me thinking.

"You do roof work after all?"

"No."

"Then what are you doing?"

"Making a mistake internally."

"Cheap."

"Not if I continue."

He smiled.

I asked, "Would you hold it?"

"With deposit."

There it was.

I did not ask how much immediately.

This was progress.

I looked at the ordinary clamps again.

One copper.

Two.

Three bits on one.

Different world.

Then back to buckle.

"How much deposit?"

"Four copper. Three days. Nonrefundable if you walk. Applied to price if you buy."

Four.

I had five.

I could reserve it.

I could reduce my liquid cash to one.

Then find fourteen more copper in three days.

Which was stupid.

Not impossible in the abstract.

Impossible under current actual opportunities.

There was a difference.

"Can deposit be extended?"

"No."

"Can balance be partial?"

"No."

"Trade?"

"Depends what."

"Used artifacts?"

"Sometimes."

Dangerous.

Holdfast.

Rest stone.

Oval.

No.

The thought arrived and I rejected it before it became accounting.

I did not know enough about any of them to trade intelligently.

And the oval was useful.

The holdfast had unresolved value.

The rest stone had unresolved system context.

Trading mystery assets for one desired object was how people became stories told by market sellers.

"No trade," I said.

"You didn't offer one."

"Correct."

He looked amused.

"Four holds it."

I stared at price card.

Eighteen.

This was the exact kind of thing I had wanted money for.

Not food.

Not rent.

Not survival.

Option value.

An object appeared.

I recognized enough to want it.

I did not have enough to act.

Five copper was suddenly both evidence of progress and completely inadequate.

That felt correct.

"Can I see demonstration?"

"Tomorrow morning if it is still here."

"Why not now?"

"Sand sack is not here."

"Bench rail is."

"And I am one person."

Fair.

"What time?"

"First bell after open."

I nodded.

Then did the difficult thing.

I left.

No deposit.

Not yet.

The seller said nothing behind me.

That made it harder.

I walked to the lamp-wick stall.

Bought two lengths.

Ordinary household money from Cala's household purse, not mine.

I did not accidentally spend Greg copper because accounting was not a fog.

The wick seller wrapped both in paper.

I put them in my shoulder bag.

Then stood there like an idiot.

The upper row continued around me.

People buying things.

Not buying things.

Arguing.

Carrying.

One woman held a little blue-glass heater to her wrist while a seller demonstrated different settings.

A man tried on a rain hood that refused to stay over his ears.

Someone laughed from a tea stall.

The world contained magic people could afford and magic they could not.

I returned home with lamp wick and no load lock.

Cala took the package.

"Right size?"

"Yes."

"Both lengths?"

"Yes."

"Good."

End of errand.

Lyssa was at her new work table.

Flat line on.

Dark fabric spread across it.

Not yesterday's customer piece.

Something else.

Her life continued being offensively separate.

I put my shoulder bag down.

Then sat.

She looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You have artifact face."

This was slander.

"I have a normal face."

"No."

Cala looked over from lamp.

"Did you buy something?"

"No."

"Good."

Tavi asked, "Did you steal something?"

"No."

"Bad."

At least one person supported me.

Lyssa put down chalk.

"What did you see?"

I considered not telling her.

Not secrecy.

Just dignity.

Then failed.

"Old harness load lock."

"What does it do?"

"Broadly, locks under load against a fixed point."

"Useful?"

"Potentially."

"Dangerous?"

"Known release problem if still loaded."

"Price?"

"Eighteen copper."

She stopped.

Good.

That was the reaction I wanted.

Cala said, "No."

"I did not ask."

"Good."

Lyssa asked, "How much do you have?"

"Five."

"Then also no."

"Excellent household support."

"You want it."

"Yes."

No point lying.

"Why?"

That was harder.

"Because the downside may fit me differently."

Lyssa looked at my crutches.

Then back at me.

"Explain without making me regret asking."

"If release needs body load reduced, most people need a partner, a support, or somewhere to sit. I already move body load onto crutches all day. Maybe that makes the release problem easier for me. Maybe."

She thought.

Not impressed.

Good.

"And if it doesn't?"

"Then it is an eighteen-copper old load lock with bad release."

"You don't have eighteen."

"Correct."

"So why are we discussing it?"

"Because I am suffering."

She smiled.

Cruel.

"Could they hold it?"

"With a deposit."

"How much?"

"Four. Three days. Nonrefundable."

"Don't."

Immediate.

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Yes."

And I did.

Four copper to buy three days would be paying almost my entire reserve for an option I had no realistic path to exercise.

That was not bold.

That was buying anxiety.

I hated correct conclusions.

Cala said, "If you find the money tomorrow, will it still be there?"

"Maybe."

"Then find money first."

There.

Not don't buy.

Sequence.

Interesting.

Lyssa looked at her.

Cala shrugged.

"He wants it."

I stared.

"You are both being strangely reasonable."

"You did not bring it home," Cala said.

Fair.

That had purchased trust.

Tavi asked, "Can it hold me to the ceiling?"

"No."

"How do you know?"

"Because if it could, eighteen copper would be cheap."

He considered this.

"Can I have eighteen copper?"

"No."

Household finance complete.

I went upstairs.

Five copper.

I counted it once.

Not because I had forgotten.

Because numbers had changed category.

Five was not reserve now.

Five was twenty-seven percent of something I wanted.

I hated percentages.

I wrote:

LOAD LOCK / USED / 18 C

HARNESS MOUNT

WORKING DEMO CLAIMED / SAND SACK

NO LOAD RATING

KNOWN: RELEASE MAY REQUIRE LOAD REDUCTION

PRE-REVISION CLAIM / MARK UNDER BODY

Then stopped.

I almost wrote:

MY CRUTCH LOAD TRANSFER MAY REDUCE DOWNSIDE

Too strong.

Changed it.

POSSIBLE: CRUTCH-SUPPORTED UNLOADING MAY CHANGE RELEASE PRACTICALITY

NOT TESTED

Better.

Then:

DEPOSIT 4 C / 3 DAYS / NONREFUNDABLE

NO.

I underlined NO once.

Then wrote:

NEED MONEY BEFORE OPTION, NOT OPTION BEFORE MONEY

That was ugly enough to be useful.

Five copper.

Twenty-six Vale.

Eighteen for the load lock.

Thirteen short.

No immediate path.

I should have felt frustrated.

I did.

But underneath that was something cleaner.

For the first time in a while, I knew exactly why I wanted more money.

Not because being poor was frightening.

Because something interesting might disappear before I could reach it.

That was a much better problem.

Still a problem.

But better.

Tomorrow morning there would be a demonstration if the load lock was still there.

I had not reserved it.

Someone else could buy it tonight.

That was the market.

I put the five copper away.

And did not go back.

# CHAPTER 350

## THE BUYER

I went back the next morning.

This was not the same as buying it.

Important distinction.

I told myself that four times before breakfast.

Then once more while putting on my shoulder bag.

Lyssa watched from the work table.

The pale flat line was already on.

She had a dark strip of cloth pinned along it.

"You are going."

"To observe."

"Of course."

"Observation is free."

"Walking uphill isn't."

"That cost is already sunk into having legs badly."

She looked at my crutches.

"Leg."

"Thank you."

Cala was cutting bread.

She did not ask where I was going.

That was worse.

I waited.

Nothing.

Finally I said, "I am not putting down the deposit."

Cala kept cutting.

"Good."

"I do understand money."

"Good."

"I am only watching the demonstration."

"Good."

This conversation had become hostile through repetition.

Tavi looked up from the floor.

"If it sticks, can they leave the sand hanging forever?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because then it becomes architecture."

He considered that.

"Can we buy architecture?"

"Not with five copper."

Lyssa said, "Apparently not with eighteen either."

Cruel household.

I left before support became fatal.

South Market upper row was already awake.

Different awake from lower market.

Less shouting.

More unlocking.

Shop shutters lifted one by one.

A glass-front place had a boy polishing the same section of window with the intensity of a man erasing evidence.

Repair lane smelled like hot metal, old rope, oil, and yesterday's rain trapped under canvas.

The old seller was there.

So was the load lock.

I noticed that first.

Blackened brass.

Three pale ceramic teeth.

Red release tab.

Eighteen-copper card.

Still mine to not own.

The seller saw me.

"You came."

"You sound disappointed."

"I did not say that."

"Is demonstration happening?"

He pointed behind the stall.

A squat iron bench stood with one end bolted to a post.

A rail ran along its side.

On the ground beside it sat a sand sack with two rope handles.

Maybe the size of a small child.

A dense child.

Good.

"After first bell," he said.

"That was the agreement."

"No agreement. You did not pay."

Fair.

I stood to one side.

Did not touch the lock.

Did not stare at it continuously.

This required effort.

A woman bought one of the ordinary brass rope guides.

Two copper.

No tragedy.

A man asked about the cracked blue-stone hinge.

Seller told him exactly what was cracked.

Man left.

Seller did not chase him.

Then first bell sounded somewhere down-market.

Not loud.

Enough.

The seller took the load lock off gray felt.

He held it by one side loop.

"Still want to see?"

"Yes."

"Then stand where you can see and not where the sack can hit you."

Excellent safety briefing.

I moved.

He threaded a wide piece of old harness webbing through the two side loops.

Not a full harness.

Demonstration strap.

The strap had a wood handle at one end.

He clipped the central jaw around the iron rail.

Not clipped.

Set.

The jaw opened when he pulled the red tab down and squeezed a small side lever I had not noticed yesterday.

Interesting.

He seated the rail between ceramic teeth.

Released tab.

Jaw closed.

No glow.

No chime.

No obvious magic.

Then he tugged strap sideways.

The lock slid six inches.

He tugged harder.

It slid another two.

Then stopped.

Not mechanical stop.

The ceramic teeth gave a faint gray-white line along their edges.

The body settled against rail.

Seller leaned his weight into strap.

Nothing moved.

There.

Working effect.

Not spectacular.

Useful.

He relaxed.

The pale edge-light faded.

He pulled red tab.

Jaw opened immediately.

Unloaded release.

"First part," he said.

"Fine."

He reset it.

Then hooked sand sack to the demonstration strap.

He did not lift the sack by hand.

A little screw hoist hung from bench post.

He turned handle until sack rose maybe a foot.

The strap angled from rail down to sack.

Load lock took weight sideways.

Gray-white line appeared along teeth.

The lock did not move.

Seller stopped hoist.

"Loaded."

"Yes."

He pulled red release tab.

It moved maybe a finger-width.

Stopped.

He did not yank harder.

Good.

"That's the defect?"

"That's the defect."

"Can you force it?"

"Probably."

"Do you?"

"No."

Also good.

"What happens if you force it?"

He looked at me.

"If I knew, the paper would say."

Fair.

He turned hoist backward.

Sand sack lowered slightly.

Not enough to touch ground.

Red tab still stuck.

Another turn.

Sack descended.

Strap angle softened.

The gray-white line along ceramic teeth dimmed.

Seller tried tab again.

Moved farther.

Still not full.

Another half turn.

The sack touched ground, but strap remained taut.

He tried.

The tab traveled almost all the way.

Jaw did not open.

Interesting.

He lowered one more fraction.

Strap sagged.

Light vanished.

Tab came down.

Jaw opened.

The lock released.

There.

Not "mostly" unloaded.

At least in this setup, nearly all.

I hated useful information.

The seller looked at me.

"That answer your question?"

"One of them."

"How many did you bring?"

"Questions?"

"Yes."

"Too many."

"Keep most."

Reasonable.

I looked at sand sack.

Then my crutches.

My theory had not died.

It had become narrower.

If the lock held me laterally while both crutches and right foot remained planted, maybe I could unload it.

If I needed the lock because one crutch had slipped, no.

If I was suspended, absolutely no.

If the anchor geometry forced me away from support, no.

If I could sit first, maybe.

If I used it as a third point while transitioning through a controlled work position, maybe.

That was still interesting.

Less magical.

More honest.

"Can you reset with load already pulling?" I asked.

"No demonstration of that."

"Seller note?"

"No."

"Any damage around the side lever?"

He looked at me.

Then at lock.

"Look."

He held it closer.

Did not hand it over.

I leaned.

The small side lever had wear polished around one edge.

Normal use.

Maybe.

The red tab had a shallow scrape on lower corner.

Could mean people kicked it.

Could mean nothing.

One ceramic tooth had a darker line near base.

Not crack that I could see.

Staining.

Old material.

I wanted a better light.

I did not ask.

The seller said, "Eighteen."

"I remember."

"Demonstration didn't lower it."

"Cruel."

"It worked."

Hostile market logic.

A voice behind me said, "Let me see it under load."

I turned.

A man stood at stall edge with a coil of yellow rope over one shoulder.

Thirty, maybe.

Heavy boots.

Roof-worker trousers with reinforced knees.

Leather waist harness rolled around one forearm.

Not rich.

Not pretending.

He looked at lock, not me.

Seller said, "Just did."

"I saw last release."

"Then you saw."

"Want to see hold."

The seller shrugged.

"One more."

He reset the demonstration.

The new man stepped closer.

"How old?"

"Pre-revision, seller said."

"Card?"

Seller showed resale note.

The man read faster than I had.

"No rating."

"No."

"Teeth replaced?"

"Not by me."

"Any slip?"

"Not on sand."

"How heavy is sand?"

"Not certified."

The man snorted.

"Nothing here is certified."

"Correct."

They liked each other less than seller liked me.

This reassured me.

The hoist raised sack again.

Lock settled.

Gray-white edge line.

The man put one hand on demonstration strap.

Not pulling.

Feeling vibration, maybe.

"Release."

Seller tried tab.

Stuck.

"Lower."

They lowered.

Same sequence.

The man watched tab instead of sack.

At near-unloaded tension he said, "Again."

Tab nearly traveled.

At slack, released.

He nodded.

Not disappointed.

That was interesting.

"You know these?" I asked.

He looked at me then.

"Old locks?"

"That release pattern."

"Enough."

Seller said, "He wants it for his crutches."

I stared at him.

"I did not say that."

"You stood here looking at your crutches."

The roof worker looked down.

Then at lock.

Then at me.

No ridicule.

Worse.

Thought.

"Positioning?"

"Maybe."

"Don't."

Direct.

I smiled slightly.

"Useful."

"I'm serious."

"I know."

He pointed at demonstration strap.

"That thing doesn't care why you're loaded. If you need it because you can't unload, you're stuck."

"Yes."

"If you can unload, why need it?"

Good question.

Not complete question.

But good.

"Controlled lateral support," I said.

He looked again.

"Maybe."

There.

Not approval.

Not dismissal.

"On flat work."

"Probably."

"With a proper harness."

"Obviously."

He looked at my missing lower left leg.

"Not hanging."

"Obviously."

"People say obviously after."

I liked him less now because he was correct.

I asked, "What would you use it for?"

He lifted rolled waist harness.

"Spare line positioner on scaffold braces. Not primary."

"Because release?"

"Because no rating."

"Release doesn't bother you?"

"It bothers me eighteen copper."

Good.

He looked at seller.

"Fifteen."

Seller said, "Eighteen."

"Sixteen."

"Eighteen."

"Old, unrated, bad release."

"Working, demonstrated, cheaper than new."

"Seventeen."

"Eighteen."

The roof worker stared at him.

Seller stared back.

No drama.

No secret signal.

Just two men who knew what a copper meant.

The roof worker looked at lock again.

Then at his harness.

Then at me, for some reason.

Maybe because I had occupied the stall like a decorative warning.

"Seventeen and the blue cord."

"Eighteen. Cord is two."

"That cord isn't worth two."

"Then don't buy cord."

I almost laughed.

The roof worker rubbed chin.

"Fine. Eighteen."

There it was.

Not theft.

Not rival.

Not villain.

A buyer.

A person with eighteen copper and a use.

The seller reached under table for paper.

I stood very still.

I had five copper.

The buyer had eighteen.

Markets were brutally literal.

He counted coins onto wood.

Not all copper.

Some silver-colored bits grouped and counted by seller.

Two larger copper pieces.

Small change.

I did not know his finances.

Maybe this was his entire reserve.

Maybe his foreman reimbursed him.

Maybe he had fifty more.

Not mine.

What mattered was that he could close.

I could not.

Seller wrote sale line.

USED LOAD LOCK / HARNESS MOUNT

NO LOAD RATING

NO LIFE-SAFETY CERTIFICATION

KNOWN LOADED-RELEASE LIMITATION

DEMONSTRATED PRIOR TO SALE

The buyer read.

Signed.

Seller signed.

Then wrapped lock in old cloth.

Not mine.

I felt stupidly angry.

Not at them.

Which made anger less useful.

The buyer tucked wrapped lock into his equipment bag.

I asked one last question.

"Why buy old one instead of newer?"

He tightened bag strap.

"Because newer is twenty-six where I buy."

Seller looked pleased at yesterday's arithmetic surviving contact with reality.

Buyer continued.

"And I need spare, not primary."

"Release problem acceptable?"

"No."

He looked at me like I had asked whether rain was acceptable.

"It's cheaper."

There.

Economics.

Not romance.

He nodded once to seller and left.

Yellow rope over shoulder.

Load lock gone.

The gray felt looked offensive without it.

Seller picked up price card.

Eighteen.

Removed it.

That was all.

No music.

No lesson.

I had watched someone else buy the thing I wanted.

This happened every day everywhere.

I hated realism.

Seller said, "You saved four copper."

I looked at him.

"By not depositing."

"Yes."

That was true.

If I had placed deposit yesterday, the lock would still be mine to fail buying in two days.

Unless I found thirteen copper.

Which I had not.

I would have purchased temporary possession of a problem.

Instead I had five.

Still.

And more information.

"Do these come through often?" I asked.

"Load locks?"

"Interesting used gear around that price."

He shrugged.

"Sometimes."

"Weekly?"

"No."

"Monthly?"

"Depends."

"On what?"

"People selling things."

Hostile epistemology.

I waited.

He sighed.

"Good used working specialty gear under twenty goes fast if somebody has a use. Over twenty sits longer unless maker is known. Broken things sit forever unless repairer knows them."

That was useful enough to cost money.

He gave it free.

I stored it.

"Artifact pieces?"

He looked at me.

"That is not one category."

Good.

"Unusual working gear."

"Same answer, fewer pieces."

"Things with unknown function?"

"Worse buyers. Worse sellers. More arguing."

"Cheaper?"

"Sometimes."

"More dangerous?"

"Sometimes."

I smiled.

"Excellent market."

"You are nineteen?"

"Unfortunately."

"You ask like you're sixty."

I went still for half a second.

Too long.

He noticed.

Of course.

Then he laughed.

"That's not praise."

Good.

I exhaled.

"Thank you."

He pointed at ordinary clamps.

"Buy something you can afford."

"Never."

"Then stop blocking customers."

Also fair.

I left.

No lock.

No deposit.

No dramatic new opportunity waiting three stalls away.

This was important.

I walked downhill.

The market felt different now.

Not because it had changed.

Because I had.

Five copper in my room was not "some money."

It was five copper.

A number inside a market where useful things appeared at eighteen and twenty-six and disappeared when actual buyers arrived.

That did not make me poor.

I had food.

House.

Work routes.

No immediate cash emergency.

It made me undercapitalized.

Much more insulting.

At home, Lyssa looked up before I said anything.

"Sold?"

"Yes."

"To you?"

"No."

She nodded once.

Not sympathy.

Good.

Cala asked, "Did you lose money?"

"No."

"Then fine."

Also good.

Tavi looked devastated.

"Ceiling thing?"

"Gone."

"Forever?"

"Probably not forever as a category."

He frowned.

"That's not what I asked."

Smart child.

"That one is gone."

He accepted tragedy better than I did.

Lyssa returned to sewing.

I stood there.

"You're not going to say anything?"

"You want me to?"

"No."

"Then no."

I went upstairs.

Five copper on shelf.

Oval where it belonged.

Rest stone boxed.

Holdfast wrapped high.

None of them had transformed into eighteen copper while I was away.

Rude.

I opened notebook.

Yesterday:

LOAD LOCK / USED / 18 C

Today I added:

DEMO OBSERVED

HOLDS SAND LOAD AGAINST RAIL

GRAY-WHITE TOOTH EDGE RESPONSE UNDER LOAD

RED RELEASE TAB STUCK UNTIL STRAP NEARLY FULLY UNLOADED

AT SLACK: RELEASE CLEAN

SELLER DID NOT FORCE TAB

NO RESET-UNDER-LOAD TEST

NO LOAD RATING

Then:

CRUTCH HYPOTHESIS NARROWER

POSSIBLE ONLY WHERE I CAN UNLOAD DELIBERATELY

NOT RESCUE

NOT FALL SUPPORT

NOT "MOBILITY" GENERALLY

GOOD.

I stared at that.

Then wrote:

SOLD 18 C TO ROOF WORKER / SPARE POSITIONING USE

He had not given name.

Good.

Then:

USED WORKING SPECIALTY GEAR <20 C MAY MOVE FAST IF USE IS CLEAR

Not law.

Seller opinion plus one example.

I added:

ONE EXAMPLE.

Old me had survived long enough to become suspicious of conclusions.

Current me was finally learning to be suspicious of wanting.

I turned page.

Money.

5 C CASH

26 C VALE

I almost wrote:

NEED 18 C

Wrong.

That was yesterday.

The object was gone.

I crossed the blank thought before it became ink.

What I needed was not eighteen copper.

I needed enough capital that the next useful thing did not become a spectator sport.

Different.

Harder.

Better.

I wrote:

CAPITAL TARGET: ENOUGH TO ACT BEFORE OPTION CLOSES

Then stopped.

Too vague.

I hated vague.

I tried again.

OPERATING RESERVE: 5 C

NEXT PURCHASE RANGE OBSERVED: ~18 C

That was factual.

Then:

DO NOT DESTROY RESERVE FOR BAD HOLD TERMS

Also factual.

Then I sat back.

The load lock had taught me something by leaving.

Annoying.

I would have preferred the object.

But that was probably why the lesson worked.

I looked at five copper.

Still mine.

Not enough.

Not nothing.

Outside, somebody shouted about onions.

Downstairs, Lyssa's flat line clicked off.

Tavi ran into something and denied it immediately.

The world continued.

I closed the notebook.

Next time, I wanted to be the buyer.

That was different from wanting to be lucky.

# CHAPTER 351

## THE DARK LINE

The flat line died halfway through a sleeve.

Lyssa pressed the brass tab again.

Nothing.

She pressed it harder.

Still nothing.

I looked up from the dining table.

"I don't think force improves electricity."

"It's not electricity."

"Then force may work."

She looked at me.

"Helpful."

I had been doing arithmetic.

Not useful arithmetic.

Five copper.

Eighteen copper.

Thirteen missing.

The load lock was gone.

The numbers remained because my brain had apparently decided grief required bookkeeping.

I closed the little scrap page.

"Wall feed?"

Lyssa checked the white braided cord.

Still plugged into the plate near the front window.

She pulled it.

Looked at the two flat contacts.

Clean enough.

Plugged it back in.

Pressed brass tab.

Nothing.

Cala came in from the kitchen side carrying a pot.

"Lamp."

We all looked at the lamp.

Dark.

It was daytime.

This made the observation less useful.

Cala put the pot down and pressed the little wall switch beside the plate.

The lamp did not answer.

"Feed," she said.

There.

Solved at household level.

Not why.

Not where.

Enough.

Lyssa looked at the dark flat-line rail.

Then at the gray-green fabric spread across her new work table.

"Of course."

"Deadline?" I asked.

"Tomorrow morning."

Not the copper-knot case.

Different work.

A short coat sleeve and two matching cuffs for somebody who apparently believed wrists required urgency.

Lyssa had been working since breakfast.

The flat-line table had been open by the window.

Daylight was good.

The pale guide line had been better.

Now she had daylight and resentment.

Cala looked toward front door.

Then upstairs.

Then at wall plate.

"Check hall plate."

I did not move.

"Me?"

"You are closest."

I was not.

Tavi was under the work table.

Again.

But he did not count as infrastructure staff.

I stood.

Crutches under arms.

Right foot.

Turn.

The hall plate sat low on the wall near front door.

Not an outlet.

A little household indicator with three tiny glazed windows.

Usually one pale green.

One clear.

One dark.

Now all three were dark.

"Dead."

Cala nodded.

"Street."

Lyssa asked, "How do you know?"

Cala pointed with chin.

"House fault leaves red. Street fault leaves nothing."

I looked at indicator.

Useful.

Household magical literacy again.

Not mine.

"Always?"

Cala looked at me.

"Usually."

Healthy answer.

I approved.

She did not need approval.

Outside, someone shouted from two houses down.

"Feed's out!"

Excellent community diagnostics.

A second voice answered.

"We know!"

Confirmed.

Tavi crawled out from under table.

"Can I touch the plate?"

"No," three adults said.

He seemed offended by coordination.

Lyssa stared at her work.

"I can still sew."

"Yes."

"I can still cut."

"Yes."

"I can still measure."

"Yes."

She looked at me.

"Stop agreeing."

"I am supporting independence."

"Go support it elsewhere."

Reasonable.

The problem was not that the work became impossible.

The problem was that one very good tool had become a normal tool in about three days.

Then disappeared.

That was how convenience betrayed you.

You survived twenty years without something.

Then used it twice.

Then its absence became injustice.

I understood Lyssa perfectly.

I had once lived without magic.

Then with S-class magic.

Then without enough magic again.

Different scale.

Same rude principle.

Cala opened the little cabinet beneath the lamp.

Inside sat spare wick.

Old wick.

One small oil bottle.

And the two fresh lengths I had bought on the upper row.

Right size.

Both lengths.

Successful errand.

"Good," she said.

This was the full reward for my heroism.

She took one fresh length out.

The wall lamp was not only feed-powered.

I had noticed the oil cup before.

Never thought about it.

Now Cala opened a side latch, turned a little brass wheel, and pulled down a shallow glass chimney.

Behind the feed-light stone sat an ordinary wick channel.

Backup.

Of course.

Carrow had experienced infrastructure before.

"Dual source?" I asked.

"Lamp," Cala said.

"That is not an answer."

"It lights."

Hostile household epistemology was spreading.

She threaded new wick through the little channel.

Tavi watched closely.

"Can I do it?"

"Next time."

"Why not this time?"

"Because I want light today."

Cruel.

Cala added oil.

Struck spark.

The lamp took flame.

Small.

Yellow.

Very ordinary.

The feed stone beside it stayed dark.

"There," she said.

Lyssa looked at daylight coming through window.

"I don't need lamp yet."

"You will."

"Probably."

"Then it is ready."

Good household planning.

I returned to dining table.

Tried to reopen my arithmetic.

Could not.

Lyssa worked without the line.

She laid a wooden straightedge over fabric.

Marked chalk.

Measured twice.

Cut.

Slower.

Not dramatically.

A little slower.

The table still helped because it was broad, stable, hers.

The magical rail was one feature.

Not the table's entire value.

Good.

I hated when objects resisted becoming metaphors correctly.

There was another shout outside.

"Marker's black at south post!"

Cala went to door.
