I followed because apparently I had become interested in outages.

Not enough to investigate.

Enough to stand at threshold.

Three neighbors were outside.

One old woman in slippers.

A man with flour on both forearms.

A girl carrying a bucket she had forgotten to put down.

At the end of our short row, near the corner, a narrow ceramic marker was fixed to a street post.

Usually it held a pale blue stripe.

Now the stripe was black.

Not dark.

Black like burned glass.

"That bad?" I asked.

Cala shrugged.

"Means closed."

"Closed by what?"

"Feed people."

"Not damage?"

"Could be damage. Could be them closing it."

Good.

Black did not mean explosion.

It meant unavailable.

The flour-armed man said, "West lane still has theirs."

Old woman said, "Then it is this branch."

Nobody panicked.

Nobody formed an adventuring party.

A boy farther down street was sent toward the junction office with two other errands.

This was apparently enough.

"How long?" Lyssa called from inside.

The old woman shouted back, "Until it comes back!"

Carrow public service announcement.

Lyssa swore softly.

I liked her more for it.

Cala went back inside.

I stayed at door another moment.

The black marker bothered me because I did not know it.

Not magically.

Practically.

In first life I had lived in places with feed grids, branch closures, overload marks, city repair systems.

Different cities.

Different eras.

Different conventions.

I remembered red flags.

Blue wax seals.

Little brass forks that rang when a branch destabilized.

A black ceramic stripe meant nothing to me beyond what Cala had just said.

That was correct.

I went inside.

No need to turn ignorance into a quest.

At midday the feed was still out.

Lyssa finished one cuff.

Then the second.

The sleeve remained.

Tavi had discovered that if he lay on floor and looked up through the work table braces, he could see the underside of the flat-line rail.

"There are marks."

Lyssa did not look.

"Do not touch them."

"I'm not."

"Then good."

I looked despite myself.

Small maker characters.

Same ones I had deliberately ignored when table arrived.

Still not my table.

Still not my problem.

Growth was repetitive.

I looked away.

Cala made lunch.

No magical cooking dependency apparent.

Pot.

Heat.

Food.

Civilization survived.

We ate at dining table while Lyssa's work table stayed open.

That was already normal now.

I noticed because I had spent an entire chapter rearranging furniture and the room had promptly stopped feeling rearranged.

That was how homes worked.

Changes became walls.

After lunch somebody knocked.

I expected feed worker.

It was not.

A neighbor girl, maybe fifteen, stood with a covered clay jar.

"My aunt says if Cala has spare lamp oil she can trade tomorrow."

Cala asked, "How much she need?"

"Just tonight."

Cala looked at our bottle.

Then at lamp.

Then poured some into a small cup.

No discussion of price.

The girl took it carefully.

"She'll bring back."

"Fine."

Door closed.

I looked at Cala.

"No copper?"

"Neighbor."

There.

Economy without market.

Annoying.

Useful.

I had spent two days thinking about liquid capital.

Five copper.

Eighteen copper.

Opportunity windows.

And Cala had just moved lamp oil across a doorway because sometimes reserve was social instead of metallic.

I did not write this down.

I wanted credit for restraint.

No one gave it.

By afternoon the light outside changed.

Clouds.

Of course.

Lyssa's work slowed more.

Not because she could not see.

Because dark cloth under gray daylight was becoming unpleasant.

Cala lit the lamp early.

Yellow light filled front half of room.

Not as bright as feed lamp usually was.

Enough.

Lyssa moved her table slightly closer.

I helped only by moving one chair out of route.

Then stopped.

No management.

No efficiency lecture.

No invention.

She knew her work.

She measured sleeve mouth.

Chalked.

Cut.

Pinned.

Unpinned one section.

Did it again.

I asked, "Would the line actually save much time here?"

"Yes."

"How much?"

She looked at me.

"Enough."

"That is a unit now?"

"Yes."

Fair.

I sat with my notes.

Not the money page.

A different page.

Old Hessa counts.

53.

53.

115.

I did not practice.

The outage did not create permission.

No independent magic.

That rule had become boring.

Good rules often did.

I put page away.

The feed came back near evening.

Not dramatically.

The hall indicator clicked once.

One glazed window turned pale green.

The street marker outside changed from black to dull blue over maybe ten seconds.

The wall lamp's feed stone gave a faint white glow even though oil flame was still burning.

Cala immediately turned wall switch off.

Then extinguished oil flame.

No ceremony.

Lyssa pressed brass tab.

The flat line returned.

Thin.

White-blue.

Perfectly straight.

She stared at it.

"I missed you."

I said, "That is how dependence starts."

"Quiet."

She aligned the sleeve.

Marked two points.

Then stopped.

"Actually."

She unplugged the table.

The line vanished.

I looked at her.

"What are you doing?"

"Checking."

She used wooden straightedge.

Measured same points.

Compared.

One mark matched.

The other differed by maybe half a finger width.

She frowned.

Plugged table in.

Line returned.

She realigned fabric.

The cloth edge itself was not straight.

There.

The line had not been wrong.

Her assumption about the edge had.

She laughed once.

Not happy.

"Good."

"What?"

"Good that feed failed?"

"No. Good that I checked."

"Ah."

She moved chalk mark.

"The line makes it easier to be confidently stupid."

I stared at her.

"That is an excellent sentence."

"Do not write it down."

I was already reaching for paper.

She pointed scissors at me.

I stopped.

Respect.

The sleeve took another hour.

Finished before full dark.

Lyssa folded it with cuffs.

Set all three pieces in a cloth wrap.

Deadline survived.

Not because magic saved her.

Not because I solved infrastructure.

Because she had a straightedge, lamp, table, experience, and enough annoyance to keep going.

The feed line was useful.

Not foundational.

That mattered.

Dinner happened with table folded.

Again.

Tavi told us he had discovered five marks under it.

Lyssa told him he had discovered the underside of furniture.

Cala asked if anyone had heard what broke the feed.

No one had.

The neighbor boy came back later and said only that junction office had closed our branch for repair and reopened it.

"Repair what?" I asked.

He shrugged.

"Didn't say."

Perfect.

No mystery earned.

No mechanism claimed.

After dinner I went upstairs.

Five copper remained where I had left it.

I counted none of it.

That was progress.

Maybe.

I opened my notes anyway.

The load lock page stared back.

SOLD.

I added nothing.

Then below the old capital line I wrote:

RESERVE IS FOR BEING ABLE TO CHOOSE

I stared at it.

Too broad.

Too wise.

Suspicious.

I crossed out BEING ABLE TO.

RESERVE IS FOR CHOICE

Still terrible.

Crossed that out too.

Finally:

KEEP 5 C FOR NOW

There.

Operational.

Not philosophy.

Downstairs, Lyssa laughed at something Tavi said.

Cala told someone to stop climbing a chair.

The wall feed hummed faintly through the plate.

Not enough to hear unless I listened.

The house had gone dark for most of a day.

Nothing important had become mine.

Nothing important had been lost.

No copper moved.

No magic count changed.

Lyssa still met her deadline.

The load lock was still gone.

Tomorrow could contain money again.

Today had contained a lamp.

Apparently that was allowed.

# CHAPTER 352

## THE COUNT

Money came the next morning.

Not thirteen copper.

Obviously.

The universe had standards.

It came as a boy carrying a blue wooden token and asking whether Greg lived here.

I was eating bread.

This felt increasingly like how employment worked.

Cala opened the door.

The boy looked maybe twelve.

Hair tied badly.

One sleeve rolled higher than the other.

Blue token in one hand.

"Greg?"

I raised bread.

"Unfortunately."

He looked at me.

Then at Cala.

Then back at me.

"Merren's counting shed needs a third hand."

I swallowed.

"Who is Merren?"

"Merren."

Excellent start.

"What counting shed?"

"South quay returns."

That was more useful.

"Why me?"

The boy held out blue token.

Stamped into it were three narrow vertical lines and a little hook at the bottom.

Not city mark I knew.

Probably house token.

"Saret said you can read tags and don't make things up."

I stopped.

There.

Ch347 had developed consequences.

Not friendship.

Not standing employment.

One sentence passed sideways through city labor.

Good.

"Saret recommended me?"

"Merren asked east gate if they knew temporary counters."

Better.

Bounded.

"What am I counting?"

"Returns."

"That word has many crimes inside it."

The boy shrugged.

"Crates. Pieces. Seals."

"Magic?"

"Some."

Dangerous answer.

Lyssa looked up from her work table.

The flat line was on again.

Civilization restored.

"Pay?" she asked.

The boy looked at her.

"Three copper. Meal. Full day."

I looked at him.

"One day?"

"One day unless second wagon comes before dark. Then maybe tomorrow too."

"Maybe is not employment."

"Merren said one day."

Good.

Three copper.

I had five.

Eight after.

Still ten short of yesterday's dead opportunity.

Which did not matter because yesterday's opportunity was dead.

This was exactly the kind of arithmetic I wanted to stop doing.

So naturally I did it immediately.

"What does third hand do?"

"Read return slips. Match crate marks. Count loose pieces. Call mismatch."

"Move crates?"

"No."

"Open sealed crates?"

"No."

"Lift anything heavier than paper?"

"Maybe small pieces."

"How small?"

He looked at my crutches.

"Merren said if you can't carry it, don't."

I liked Merren already.

Cala asked, "Where?"

"South quay. Blue shed behind return wall."

"Before what bell?"

"Second."

I looked at bread.

Then at boy.

"I need ten minutes."

"I can wait outside."

"You do not need to."

"I have two more tokens."

Ah.

Not special.

Wonderful.

He left.

Lyssa said, "Three copper."

"I heard."

"You are going."

"Probably."

"Why probably?"

"Because I am trying not to become a man who accepts every coin-shaped sound."

Cala said, "It is counting."

"Yes."

"You can count."

"Usually."

"Then go count."

Household strategic council complete.

I finished bread.

Did not take the rest stone.

Did not take holdfast.

Did not take anything magical except the oval already living in the shoulder bag.

I considered removing it.

Then left it.

Different job.

No route restriction.

No reason to dramatize bag contents.

Five copper stayed upstairs.

The South quay smelled like wet rope, river mud, old wood, and commerce trying to hide all three.

I had been near it before.

Not this return yard.

The main quay ran long and loud beside dark water.

Loaders moved sacks.

Porters shouted numbers.

A narrow-bellied freight boat sat low under bundled reed mats.

Two cranes turned slowly over the water.

Not magical cranes.

Wood, rope, men.

One had a little pale-stone brake plate near its drum.

Magic used where useful.

Not everywhere.

Good.

The return wall was exactly that.

A long brick wall separating the main quay from a yard filled with goods that had apparently disappointed somebody.

Blue shed behind it.

Three vertical white lines painted on door.

Same as token.

I handed token to a woman at a waist-high desk.

Not Merren.

She had narrow eyes, dark braid, and a pencil tucked behind one ear.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

She looked at crutches.

Then at a stool beside desk.

"Sit when you need. Do not block lane."

"Excellent."

"Merren is inside."

The blue shed was larger than expected.

Not one room.

A roof over three open bays.

Bay one had sealed crates.

Bay two had open crates and sorting tables.

Bay three held loose equipment arranged in ugly rows.

Hooks.

Lamp housings.

Ceramic feed plates.

Lengths of braided conduit.

Two boxes of brass contact tongues.

Ordinary magical infrastructure pieces.

Some cracked.

Some clean.

Some still carrying chalk tags.

I felt my brain lean forward.

No.

Work first.

A thick man in a blue vest waved me over.

Gray beard cut short.

One knee wrapped under trousers.

Merren.

Probably.

"You read?"

"Yes."

"Fast?"

"Usually."

"Add?"

"Dangerous question."

He did not smile.

"Saret says you stop when tag is wrong instead of deciding what it meant."

That was oddly flattering.

"That is true."

"Good."

He handed me a board.

Three sheets clipped to it.

Top headings:

RETURN LOT
SOURCE
SEAL
EXPECTED
FOUND
HOLD

"Today is municipal returns and contractor rejects."

"Difference?"

"Source line."

"Not procedure?"

"Not for you."

Excellent.

"My job?"

"Bay two. Ressa opens. Durn counts physical. You read expected and mark found after Durn calls. If count differs, do not fix arithmetic. Mark HOLD and call me."

"Why three people?"

Merren looked at me.

"Because one person lies to himself. Two people agree with each other. Three people annoy everyone."

I smiled.

"Institutional wisdom."

"Also insurer requires it on sealed returns over ten copper book value."

There.

Actual reason.

"Do I know book value?"

"No."

"Do I need to?"

"No."

"Beautiful."

He pointed at a chalk line painted across floor.

"Stay this side unless Ressa says move. Fork carts cross behind you."

"Fork carts?"

A low four-wheeled platform rolled past.

No animal.

A man walked behind it holding a long brass handle.

The platform moved with a quiet hum.

Two pale stones sat under its rear axle.

Not autonomous.

Assisted.

"Those."

"Understood."

"If bell rings twice, stop counting."

"Why?"

"Cart crossing."

"If bell rings three?"

"Feed fault."

"Four?"

"Fire."

"Five?"

Merren stared.

"If someone rings five, leave."

Fair.

Ressa turned out to be the narrow-eyed woman from front desk.

Durn was younger than me by maybe two years and built like every barrel had personally insulted him.

He wore fingerless gloves.

"Crutch man?"

"Counting man today."

"Good. I hate writing."

"I hate lifting."

Partnership achieved.

First crate was easy.

Return lot 41.

Source: North Lamp Cooperative.

Seal mark matched.

Expected: twelve feed-switch housings.

Ressa cut seal.

Opened lid.

Durn counted.

"Twelve."

I marked FOUND 12.

No hold.

Second crate.

Eight ceramic wall plates.

Eight found.

Third.

Twenty brass contact tongues.

Durn counted nineteen.

I looked at expected.

Twenty.

"Again," I said.

He counted.

"Nineteen."

Ressa looked at me.

I marked FOUND 19.

HOLD.

Called Merren.

He came.

Did not accuse anyone.

Did not look under table dramatically.

He checked seal strip.

Checked source line.

Asked Ressa whether seal was intact before cut.

"Yes."

Asked Durn count.

"Nineteen twice."

Asked me expected.

"Twenty."

Merren wrote one note.

Moved crate to side.

"Next."

That was it.

No missing-tongue conspiracy.

No secret smuggling ring.

Just nineteen where paper said twenty.

I respected this job.

By midmorning we had twelve lots done.

Three holds.

One because count mismatch.

One because the seal mark had an extra notch not on slip.

One because a returned heating plate was the right model but wrong size.

That last one caused Durn to argue.

"It says six-inch."

"It is six-inch."

Ressa put a little brass gauge over it.

The plate did not fit.

"Seven."

Durn looked offended.

"Looks six."

I said, "This is why civilization invented gauges."

He looked at me.

"You always talk like that?"

"Unfortunately."

Ressa marked hold.

At first rest, I sat outside bay on stool.

South quay moved around us.

Fork cart bell twice.

People stopped lane.

Cart crossed.

Then work resumed.

No one explained the entire port to me.

Good.

Merren brought meal packets.

Bread.

Soft cheese.

Half an apple.

Apparently temporary labor across Carrow had standardized around food wrapped in paper.

I supported this policy.

He sat on a crate across from me.

"You worked east gate one day?"

"One."

"Why not stay?"

"It was one-day work."

"Good answer."

"Was there another?"

"People hear 'city gate' and invent careers."

I understood.

He ate.

I asked, "How often do you need temporary counters?"

Merren looked at me.

I immediately regretted sounding eager.

Capital face.

Terrible.

"Depends on returns."

"Weekly?"

"No."

"Monthly?"

"Sometimes twice. Sometimes none."

"Standing list?"

"Yes."

There.

Not a job.

Access.

Potentially more useful.

"How does someone get on it?"

"Finish day without making me regret token."

Reasonable.

"Pay always three?"

"For counters. More for seal clerks."

"What does seal clerk require?"

"Training. Bond. Two references."

Not today.

Good.

"Fork handler?"

"Training."

"Bay lead?"

"Years."

"Merren?"

"Bad decisions."

I smiled.

He continued eating.

This was useful hierarchy.

Not because I wanted to climb it.

Because money had structure.

Three copper for one day of accurate counting.

More pay attached to more trust, more training, more liability.

Exactly as it should.

I did not ask how much seal clerks made.

Restraint.

Afternoon wagon arrived late.

Not second wagon.

The first one had apparently been delayed.

This meant our one-day work still had work inside it.

We moved to bay three.

Loose equipment returns.

These were worse.

Not because magical.

Because loose.

Expected sheets described families, counts, condition grades.

Twelve conduit clamps, grade B.

Six lamp stones, inactive.

Three regulator cups, cracked.

Eight wall indicators, housing intact.

Nothing rare.

Nothing mysterious.

Mostly city hardware that had spent enough time in walls to become ugly.

One regulator cup caught my attention.

Dark bronze.

Three vent channels.

A little spiral scoring around base.

Old first-life recognition flickered.

Not artifact.

Not treasure.

A common branch regulator style from somewhere I had known later.

Maybe earlier model.

Maybe not.

I looked at slip.

EXPECTED: 3 REGULATOR CUPS / CRACKED / SCRAP HOLD

Durn counted three.

I marked three.

That was all.

This job did not pay me to become interested.

Next lot.

Then something actually happened.

Not magical.

Worse.

Durn said, "Four."

Expected said five.

I looked at table.

Four small white ceramic fuse sleeves.

Ressa said, "Again."

Durn counted.

"Four."

I marked found four.

Hold.

Called Merren.

Before he arrived, a man from another bay leaned over chalk line.

Blue apron.

"That lot came open. One was broken in wagon."

Useful information.

Dangerous timing.

I looked at slip.

Seal column blank.

Open return.

He continued.

"Just mark four. Break was known."

"I did mark four."

"Then no hold."

"Expected says five."

"Because slip was written before wagon."

Could be true.

Probably was.

Not my authority.

"Merren can clear it."

The man sighed.

"You're making work."

Durn looked at me.

Ressa did not.

She was very carefully not helping.

Good.

"Yes," I said. "Three people annoy everyone."

Merren arrived.

Blue-apron man explained.

Merren asked, "Break tag?"

The man paused.

"Driver had it."

"Where?"

"Don't know."

Merren looked at me.

"Expected?"

"Five."

"Found?"

"Four."

"Hold stays."

He walked away.

Blue-apron man swore quietly.

Not at me exactly.

At process.

I understood.

Five minutes later a driver brought a little red break tag.

Lot number matched.

Merren clipped it to sheet.

Cleared hold.

No one died.

No theft uncovered.

Paper caught up with reality.

That was apparently worth three copper.

Near final bell my shoulders were tired.

Not injured.

Just tired from standing, sitting, writing, moving between bays, holding myself upright longer than a household day.

I took rest when available.

Did not perform endurance for money.

Durn noticed once.

"You good?"

"Yes."

"Need stool?"

"I have stool."

He looked at stool beside me.

"Right."

Excellent medical care.

We finished thirty-one return lots.

Seven had holds at some point.

Five cleared with additional paper or recount.

Two remained held when day ended.

I did not know what happened to them.

Not my shift anymore.

Merren took my board.

Read sheets.

Checked my totals.

Not all of them.

Enough.

Then handed me three copper.

Actual coins.

One.

Two.

Three.

"You available if token comes again?"

There.

Not promise.

Not schedule.

"Usually."

"I will put name on temp list."

"Good."

"Doesn't mean work."

"I know."

"Doesn't mean same pay if job differs."

"Also good."

He looked at me.

"You like rules."

"I like knowing which ones exist."

"Same problem."

Maybe.

I put three copper in inner pouch.

Eight total now.

Not eighteen.

Not enough to recover yesterday.

There was nothing to recover.

Eight was simply more than five.

That needed to be enough.

On way home I passed a little street cart selling fried dough.

One bit.

I stopped.

Not because hungry.

Because I had money.

Dangerous.

One bit was not one copper.

Still money.

I looked at cart.

Then kept walking.

Not deprivation.

I had eaten.

I did not want fried dough enough.

This distinction mattered to me now.

At home Lyssa was folding finished coat pieces.

Cala had lamp open for some reason unrelated to yesterday's outage.

Tavi was trying to stack three cups.

Normal life.

Lyssa looked at me.

"How much?"

"Three."

"Good."

"I am now forty-four percent of a thing I cannot buy."

She stared.

"You need a different hobby."

"It is gone. I know."

"Then stop dividing yourself by it."

Fair.

I sat.

Shoulders complained quietly.

Cala asked, "Work again tomorrow?"

"Maybe if second wagon came. It didn't. Merren put me on temp list."

"Good."

"Not standing work."

"Still good."

There.

That was probably the right frame.

Not income solved.

Access improved.

One person now knew I could do accurate temporary counting.

That could matter later.

Or never.

Three copper mattered now.

I went upstairs after dinner.

Five old copper.

Three new.

Eight.

I counted once.

Allowed.

Then opened notes.

I wrote:

SOUTH QUAY RETURNS

TEMP COUNTER / 3 C + MEAL

NO STANDING WORK

TEMP LIST

Then:

3-PERSON COUNTING ON SEALED RETURNS

EXPECTED != FOUND -> HOLD

DO NOT FIX PAPER YOURSELF

That last one amused me.

Then money.

8 C

VALE 26 C

I stared at eight.

The urge arrived immediately.

How far to twenty?

How far to thirty?

What kind of opportunity did eight unlock?

What did fifteen unlock?

I almost started a table.

Stopped.

The load lock had taught one lesson correctly.

Capital mattered.

It did not mean every copper needed a destiny before I earned it.

So beneath eight I wrote:

KEEP IT LIQUID

That sounded annoyingly sophisticated.

I crossed out LIQUID.

KEEP IT

Better.

Then I added one more line.

MONEY CAN COME FROM BEING BORING CORRECTLY

I stared at it.

Too good.

Too broad.

Possibly true.

I left it.

Downstairs someone dropped a cup.

Tavi shouted that it was not broken.

Cala shouted back that this was not the same as success.

Lyssa laughed.

I put eight copper away.

Tomorrow did not owe me another three.

That was fine.

Today had paid.

# CHAPTER 353

## THE BAD LANTERN

Eight copper was enough money to become annoying.

Not rich.

Not comfortable.

Annoying.

At five copper, most interesting things solved themselves by being impossible.

At eight, I had to make decisions.

I discovered this while standing in the Lower Steps with a lantern in my hand that did not produce light.

"Six," the woman said.

I looked at the lantern.

"It is bad at being a lantern."

"Five, then."

That was not how I expected bargaining to work.

I looked at her.

She was perhaps forty, broad through the shoulders, with gray beginning at both temples and three stacked wooden trays at her feet.

Not a stall.

Not a shop.

A building clearance.

The narrow house behind her had its front door open and half its life on the street.

Chairs.

Two bed frames.

A cracked wash basin.

Cook pots.

A bundle of curtain rods.

Three lamps.

One small household warming plate with the core removed.

And the lantern.

I had not come to buy anything.

I had come for onions.

This was why planning was useless.

"Why five?" I asked.

"Because you insulted it."

"That usually raises the price."

"I want it gone."

Good answer.

The lantern was squat and ugly.

Dark brass body.

Square glass on four sides.

Handle wrapped in old black cord.

No flame cup.

No wick.

A thumb-sized cloudy stone sat behind the rear plate.

The front shutter had three positions marked by shallow cuts.

Open.

Half.

Closed.

Except open did nothing.

The woman had demonstrated that already.

She pressed the rear stud.

Stone warmed faintly.

Glass stayed dark.

Then she turned the front shutter to half.

Still dark.

Closed.

Still dark.

"Registered?"

"No current registration. Household light."

"Old paper?"

She pointed to one tray.

"Somewhere in that if my brother didn't throw it away."

"Maker mark?"

She turned lantern over.

A shallow stamp under base.

Three reeds inside a circle.

I knew it.

Not the maker.

The family.

Or I thought I did.

That was worse.

First life.

A long time later.

Camp equipment.

Cheap field shelters.

People complaining because the newer ones were lighter and the old ones made everything look sick.

Not light.

That was the wrong memory.

I looked through one dark pane.

"Can I see it active again?"

"You saw it."

"I saw the stone warm."

"That's active."

Maybe.

"Can we go inside?"

She narrowed her eyes.

"Why?"

There.

Do not tell seller the question.

"Shade. Hard to see weak light out here."

Reasonable.

True enough.

She shrugged.

"Front room. Don't touch anything else."

I followed her in.

The room was nearly empty.

One table.

Dust rectangles where furniture had been.

Curtains already down.

Bright daylight came through bare window.

Not enough.

"Darker room?"

"You buying a lantern or renting my house?"

"Depends on lantern."

She sighed.

Led me down a short hall.

Back room had no window.

Door open gave a stripe of daylight.

She held lantern.

Pressed rear stud.

Cloudy stone warmed.

No light.

I watched the room.

Not lantern.

Nothing.

"Close door?"

"No."

Fair.

"Mostly?"

She pulled it until only a hand-width gap remained.

Room dimmed.

Lantern remained dark.

But the stripe of light from hall changed.

Not much.

I moved my head.

The wall opposite the gap looked flatter.

Wrong word.

Less bright around the stripe.

I looked at my right hand.

Then lantern.

Then the floor.

Memory arrived properly.

Oh.

Oh, you ugly little bastard.

Three Reeds was not a lamp family.

Not originally.

They made old glare-cutters.

Field signal control.

You put one near a map table or shuttered shelter and it ate spill.

Later models paired with actual light sources.

People called the whole combined unit a lantern because people were lazy and history was cruel.

This one might be only the cutter half.

Or broken.

Or both.

I did not know exact model.

I knew category.

Maybe.

Important word.

Maybe.

"Stud again?"

She pressed it off.

The hallway stripe seemed brighter at its edges.

Subtle.

On.

Edges dulled.

Not darkness.

Not concealment.

Not light removal from whole room.

Something around contrast.

Spill.

Glare.

Maybe.

I kept my face boring.

This was difficult because my face had just become economically interested.

"Still doesn't light," she said.

"No."

True.

"Five."

"You said six outside."

"Then you insulted it."

Right.

"Why are you clearing house?"

She looked at me.

Wrong question.

"Never mind."

"Brother's moving north. House sold. Half this was our mother's."

I did not ask more.

Good.

"Did she use this?"

"Cupboard."

"Ever work?"

"When I was little I remember it making light. Might be remembering another lamp."

Useful uncertainty.

"Any repairs?"

"Not by me."

"Dropped?"

"Probably. It's old."

"Stone replaced?"

"Don't know."

"Can I look for paper?"

"If you put everything back."

I sat on the floor because balancing over a tray while using crutches was stupid.

The paper tray contained receipts, household marks, two dead warranty slips, a folded recipe for something involving pears, and one narrow brown card with Three Reeds circle at top.

Not for this exact lantern.

General household instruction card.

I read only what was there.

THREE REEDS SHADE UNIT

FOR TABLE / WALL / CARRIED SERVICE

KEEP VENTS CLEAR

DO NOT ENCLOSE ACTIVE CORE

PAIR ONLY WITH RATED LAMP ASSEMBLY

There.

Shade unit.

Not lantern.

Below that, half the writing was water-stained.

One surviving line:

...REDUCES STRAY THROW BEYOND SET FACE...

My pulse moved.

Set face.

Front shutter positions.

Not illumination.

Directional spill control.

Maybe.

The card did not identify model.

Did not prove condition.

Did not prove this unit matched card beyond maker family.

But the market problem was now obvious.

The woman was not stupid.

She had an old household object remembered as a lantern, with no current registration, no exact paper, no visible light output, and a core that merely warmed.

Its useful effect was subtle in daylight.

Five copper was not nothing for a thing that appeared broken.

And if it really was an old shade unit, five might be excellent.

Not because it secretly shot lightning.

Because I knew a use.

Theatre.

No.

Not theatre specifically.

Any place where controlling where light did not go mattered.

Camp.

Night travel.

Windows.

Signals.

Observation.

Reading without advertising the reader.

Maybe paired with another lamp.

Maybe carried.

Maybe mounted.

And the downside was already on the card.

Pair only with rated lamp assembly.

Meaning this thing might be useless alone.

Worse, active core produced heat.

Vents clear.

Do not enclose.

So the obvious temptation to wrap it and make a perfect dark box could cook the core or me.

Excellent.

A useful object with instructions against the first stupid thing I wanted to do.

I folded the card exactly where it had been.

"This card goes with it?"

"If you buy it."

"Five?"

"Five."

I had eight.

Three after.

Exactly where I had been before yesterday's work.

That annoyed me.

But cash was not the only thing that counted anymore.

If I bought it, I would have three copper, twenty-six Vale, a holdfast I did not understand, a retired rest stone I had not tested, an oval with bad boundaries, and now a possibly functional shade unit that required compatible lighting hardware I did not own.

This was beginning to resemble a collection assembled by a man who hated convenience.

I looked at the woman.

"Four."

"Five."

"No exact paper."

"Card."

"Family card. Not model paper."

She looked at card.

Then me.

"You know what it is?"

Danger.

"I know the card says shade unit."

"So it works?"

"I don't know."

True.

"You wanted darker room."

"Because weak effects are easier to see in dark rooms."

Also true.

She stared at me.

I waited.

"Five."

She was not moving.

Good seller.

I could walk.

That mattered.

Five copper was sixty-two and a half percent of my cash.

I hated percentages when they were mine.

I looked at the unit again.

The old Three Reeds family had survived into my first life mostly as jokes about ugly military lighting.

That did not mean this specific object worked.

It did mean the family was real enough in my memory that I was not inventing value from one stained card.

I wanted it.

There.

Not curiosity.

Not maybe.

I wanted it.

Because if it worked, even narrowly, it changed night.

Not by making night brighter.

By letting me decide where brightness leaked.

That was a different operating rule.

"Five," I said.

She held out hand.

I paid.

Three copper remained.

Again.

This time it felt different.

Not poorer.

Converted.

Cash into a question with handles.

She gave me the brown family card.

Then found a cloth sack.

"Don't put it in sack active," I said.

She paused.

Looked at card.

"Vents."

"Yes."

"You did know something."

"I can read."

"Saret was right."

I froze.

"You know Saret?"

"Who?"

Right.

My brain had become socially diseased.

"Nobody."

She handed me sack.

I put inactive shade unit inside.

It fit shoulder bag badly but better than holdfast.

Handle protruded.

I wrapped it in cloth so brass did not beat against my hip.

The onions were forgotten.

I remembered them halfway home.

Turned around.

Bought onions.

I did not count the bit-level price because civilization had progressed.

At home, Lyssa saw the handle sticking from my bag.

"No."

"Hello."

"You bought something."

"Technically."

"How much?"

Cala looked over from kitchen.

I considered lying.

Not seriously.

"Five copper."

Silence.

Tavi said, "That's a lot."

Betrayed by youth.

Lyssa put both hands flat on work table.

"Yesterday you made three."

"Correct."

"Today you spent five."

"Also correct."

"On what?"

I took out the shade unit.

Set it on table inactive.

"A bad lantern."

Lyssa closed her eyes.

Cala said, "Take it upstairs."

"It is inactive."

"Still upstairs."

Reasonable artifact policy.

I showed them card first.

Lyssa read.

"Shade unit."

"Yes."

"So not a lantern."

"Probably."

"Does it work?"

"Possibly."

"Five copper."

"You keep returning to that."

"Because it is five copper."

Fair.

I explained only what I had observed.

Core warmed.

No emitted light.

In darker room, hallway spill seemed to flatten at edges when active.

Family card said reduces stray throw beyond set face.

No exact model paper.

No proof of rated lamp compatibility.

No test beyond seller demonstration.

Lyssa listened.

Then pointed at front shutter.

"Set face."

"Maybe."

"And this turns it?"

"Maybe."

"You don't know anything."

"I know more than when I bought the holdfast."

"That is not comforting."

Cala asked, "Danger?"

"Heat if active. Vents must stay clear. Card says don't enclose active core. Pair only with rated lamp assembly."

"Do you have rated lamp assembly?"

"No."

Cala pointed upstairs.

"Wonderful."

I took it upstairs.

Did not activate it.

That was important.

I had already seen enough to justify the purchase decision.

Home was not test bench.

Also I had no compatible lamp.

I put the shade unit on a different shelf from holdfast.

Not above Tavi reach merely in theory.

Actually above it.

Family card beneath.

Then I sat.

Three copper.

Again.

But now I owned something I had wanted strongly enough to spend five.

That was new.

The load lock had taught me I could want and lose.

This taught me I could want and choose.

I took out scrap.

Wrote:

THREE REEDS SHADE UNIT / 5 C

NO EXACT MODEL PAPER

FAMILY CARD ONLY

CORE WARMS / NO LIGHT OUTPUT OBSERVED

DARK ROOM: POSSIBLE REDUCTION OF STRAY LIGHT EDGE / NOT MEASURED

CARD: REDUCES STRAY THROW BEYOND SET FACE

KEEP VENTS CLEAR

DO NOT ENCLOSE ACTIVE CORE

PAIR ONLY WITH RATED LAMP ASSEMBLY

NO HOME TEST

Then:

3 C CASH

26 C VALE

I looked at those numbers.

Did not write KEEP IT.

That instruction had lasted one day.

Good instructions were allowed to expire when the world changed.

I added:

FIND COMPATIBLE LIGHT CONTEXT BEFORE TEST

Then stopped.

No price target.

No immediate shopping list.

No plan to drag it to Mara.

I knew the broad family better than that.

What I needed was not appraisal.

I needed a lamp.

Not to own.

To borrow, rent, observe, or access safely.

That was a different problem.

Downstairs, Lyssa called, "Did you at least get the onions?"

I looked at the sack beside my chair.

"Yes."

"Good."

A pause.

"Then you're only mostly stupid."

I smiled.

Five copper for a bad lantern.

Three left.

Twenty-six owed.

And for the first time in a while, the thing I wanted next was not more money.

It was the right kind of darkness.

# CHAPTER 354

## THE HIGH SHELF

The problem with putting dangerous things out of a child's reach was that children considered reach a temporary condition.

I learned this the next morning.

Tavi had moved a chair.

Not toward the shade unit.

That would have been simpler.

He moved it toward a spool of red string on the shelf below it.

I came upstairs and found him standing on the chair with one foot on the seat, one knee against the back, both hands reaching upward, and his entire body arranged according to the engineering principle of eventually falling.

"No."

He froze.

Not guilty.

Offended by interruption.

"I need the red one."

"Get down."

"But I can almost get it."

"That is not a supporting argument."

"If I just move the chair little more, I can."

"That is the opposite of a supporting argument."

He looked over his shoulder.

The chair shifted under him.

I moved faster than dignity preferred.

One crutch planted.

Other crutch sideways against the chair leg.

Right hand on his shirt at the back.

"Down."

"I'm not falling."

"You have begun negotiating with gravity. Down."

He climbed down.

I moved the chair away.

Then looked up.

The Three Reeds shade unit sat on the high shelf.

Inactive.

Family card beneath it.

The holdfast sat farther over, wrapped, ring sideways.

Rest stone boxed on the lower high shelf.

All of them supposedly out of Tavi reach.

Except the red string had been out of Tavi reach too.

This was a useful demonstration.

I hated useful demonstrations.

"Why did you need red string?"

He held up the spoon carrier.

The carrier had developed wheels.

Not good wheels.

Two wooden buttons threaded on a little stick beneath one side.

One button was larger than the other.

The spoon still sat in the center like a passenger who had made several bad life choices.

"Blue string bends too much. Red string is stronger."

"Is it?"

"It looks stronger."

Excellent materials science.

"Ask next time."

"You were downstairs."

"Then ask downstairs."

"But I was upstairs."

There it was.

Tavi did not have Greg-style dry answers.

He had logistics.

Messy, sincere logistics.

"You moved a chair to climb near things you are not allowed to touch."

"I wasn't touching those."

He pointed upward.

"I know."

"I was touching string."

"I know."

"So why are you mad about the other things?"

I opened my mouth.

Closed it.

Because his question was not actually stupid.

"I'm not mad about you touching them. You didn't. I'm saying the shelf is not safe enough if you can get that close by moving a chair."

He looked at chair.

Then shelf.

"I can move bigger chair too."

"Thank you, Tavi."

"The kitchen one is taller."

"Please stop helping."

"And the little table if I put it on the chair."

"Absolutely not."

He frowned.

"I didn't do it."

"Good. Preserve that record."

Cala appeared at the top of the stairs.

She looked at chair.

Looked at Tavi.

Looked at me.

Then looked at the shelf.

She did not ask what happened.

This was because she had eyes.

"Downstairs," she told Tavi.

"But red string."

"I will get the string. Downstairs."

He went.

Not happily.

Not dramatically.

He took the spoon carrier with him and narrated its wheel problem all the way down.

"The left one is not really left because when it turns around then it is the right one but it still sticks and if I use red string I think the spoon can pull it instead of the chair pulling it because the chair is too heavy and also I need another button but not the big button because that makes it crooked."

His voice faded.

Cala watched him go.

Then pointed at the shelf.

"No."

I leaned on both crutches.

"I agree."

That surprised her enough to pause.

"Good."

"I would like credit for agreeing quickly."

"No."

Reasonable.

She walked to the shelf but did not touch anything.

"What happens if one falls?"

"Depends which."

"That answer means no."

"The shade unit is inactive. The holdfast is inactive. The rest stone is boxed."

"What happens if one falls?"

I looked at them.

The honest answer was irritating.

"I don't know."

"Then they do not live above where people walk."

Simple.

Correct.

I had been thinking about reach.

Not drop path.

Different problem.

"Agreed."

"And not above your clothes."

"Why clothes?"

"Because if something leaks, heats, stains, sparks, or does whatever old magical rubbish does, I do not want your shirts becoming part of the test."

Cala had more imagination than I preferred.

"Also agreed."

She looked around my room.

This was not a large room.

Bed.

Chair.

Small table.

Shelf.

Clothes.

Shoulder bag.

Crutches when I was not using them.

Several decades of first-life experience had not prepared me for the storage consequences of being nineteen and economically irresponsible in a small room.

"Where?" I asked.

"Not my problem."

Then she stopped at the door.

"Actually it is partly my problem because this is my house."

"I was going to say that."

"Too late. Find somewhere safe before dinner."

She left.

I looked at the shelf.

Three objects.

Four if I counted the oval, which lived near my bag because I carried it often.

I counted the oval.

Of course I counted the oval.

The problem had stopped being ownership.

It was inventory.

Not market inventory.

Physical inventory.

Where things existed when I was not thinking about them.

This had not mattered when I owned one weird object.

Then two.

Then three.

Apparently four was where a home became a warehouse with opinions.

I moved the red string first.

Downstairs.

Tavi accepted it without comment because he had already moved on to attaching the wrong button.

Then I spent an hour discovering that our household contained many storage spaces and almost none of them belonged to me.

Kitchen cabinet.

No.

Cala did not even have to say it.

Under bed.

Too easy for Tavi.

Top of wardrobe.

Same chair problem, plus drop path.

Behind clothes chest.

Damp wall.

No.

Inside clothes chest.

Clothes.

No.

Shoulder bag.

Could not fit holdfast and I needed the bag for ordinary life.

I briefly considered wrapping the objects in bedding and storing them in a corner.

Then remembered the sentence DO NOT ENCLOSE ACTIVE CORE and became angry at an inactive object for limiting imaginary storage methods.

It was inactive.

Enclosure was probably fine while inactive.

Probably.

I did not enjoy probably enough to build a household system around it.

Lyssa came in during this process carrying a folded length of dark cloth.

She stopped.

My bed had become an artifact staging area.

Holdfast wrapped.

Rest-stone box.

Shade unit inactive.

Family card.

Oval wrapped separately.

Three ordinary cloth pieces labeled in charcoal because I had apparently become a clerk in my own room.

"What are you doing?"

"Failing storage."

She put her cloth on chair.

"Why are they all out?"

"Tavi moved a chair."

She looked at shelf.

Then at me.

"Did he touch anything?"

"No. Red string."

"Ah."

That was all.

She understood immediately.

"Cala said they can't stay high."

"Also drop path."

"Yes."

"You both have become safety professionals overnight."

"You bought five copper of old magic yesterday."

"One old magic."

"You already had the other old magics."

"Different old magics."

She sat on the chair.

"That does not improve the sentence."

I looked around again.

"Do you have any idea?"

Lyssa considered.

Not instantly.

That mattered.

She did not exist to solve Greg problems.

"There is the cedar chest in the back room," she said eventually.

"Cala's winter things."

"Not that one. The smaller one under the folded frames."

I did not know there was a smaller cedar chest under the folded frames.

This was because other people had lives.

"What's in it?"

"Nothing useful. Old account books, broken curtain hooks, some lamp paper, probably dust."

I looked at her.

"Lamp paper?"

She looked at me.

"No."

"I only reacted to noun."

"You reacted with your entire body."

"What lamp paper?"

"I don't know. Old household papers. I saw a lamp drawing once when I was looking for a buckle pattern. It could be a receipt. It could be instructions for a lamp we do not own. It could be somebody drawing a lamp because they were bored."

"Interesting."

"Storage first."

There.

Lyssa was getting better at managing my brain despite not being my manager.

Dangerous.

"Does chest lock?"

"There is a key hole."

"Key?"

"Ask Cala."

I went downstairs.

Cala was cutting onions.

My onions.

At least the purchase had generated household value.

"There is a smaller cedar chest in the back room?"

She did not look up.

"Yes."

"Does it lock?"

"Maybe."

"Do we have the key?"

"Maybe."

"You sound like me."

"That should frighten you."

She finished one onion.

Wiped knife.

Went to a drawer.

Not a key drawer.

A drawer containing string, chalk, three small nails, folded paper, a spoon with no matching set, and at least seven things that proved every household eventually became Tool Lane.

She moved two items.

Found a small iron key.

"Try this."

"Why do you know where that is?"

"Because I live here."

A devastating explanation.

The back room was mostly household overflow.

Folded bed frame against one wall.

Two baskets.

A rolled mat.

Broken stool that had somehow earned storage instead of execution.

And beneath two folded wood frames, a small cedar chest bound at corners with dark iron.

Not magical.

I checked.

Not because I had reason.

Because my life had become like this.

Lyssa helped me move the folded frames.

Not the chest.

I could not safely move the frames alone while on crutches.

She took one end.

I used one crutch and one hand on the other.

Awkward.

Slow.

No speech about independence.

Just furniture.

Chest lid opened.

Dust.

Account books.

Curtain hooks.

Two old lamp chimneys wrapped in paper.

A packet of household instruction cards tied with faded blue string.

I stared.

Lyssa said, "Storage."

"You are cruel."

"Storage."

We emptied the chest onto a low table.

Not discarded.

Sorted.

Account books stayed together.

Hooks into a small box.

Lamp chimneys to upper household shelf.

Instruction cards remained tied.

I did not open them.

This was a physical act of maturity so painful I expected applause.

None came.

The key fit.

Turned with resistance.

Lock caught.

Good.

Cala inspected chest.

"Where will it sit?"

"Back wall."

"Not against wall."

"Damp?"

"Yes."

We set two ordinary wood blocks beneath and behind it so air could move.

Then came the harder question.

What went inside?

Not the oval.

I carried it enough that locking it away defeated purpose.

Also its known behavior was not active danger while wrapped.

Probably.

Again.

I hated that word.

The rest stone stayed boxed.

Into chest.

The holdfast stayed wrapped, ring sideways.

Into chest, with cloth around base so it could not roll or strike the stone box.

The shade unit went in last.

Inactive.

I hesitated.

"Card says don't enclose active core," I said.

Cala looked at me.

"Is it active?"

"No."

"Are you going to activate it in the chest?"

"No."

"Then put it in the chest."

I did.

But not wrapped tight.

Set upright.

Handle folded.

Vents unobstructed anyway because habits were cheap.

Family card went into a separate paper envelope on top, not under the object where oil or heat residue might damage it later.

Lyssa noticed.

"You are already planning for it to leak."

"I am planning for paper to remain paper."

"You bought a bad lantern and became a librarian."

"Artifact ownership has layers."

"You have problems."

"Also layers."

Cala said, "Key."

I handed it to her.

She looked at my hand.

"Why are you giving it to me?"

"Because if I keep it in my room, Tavi can find it."

"He can find things in my room too."

Fair.

"Then where?"

She thought.

This became a household security conference about a key the size of my smallest finger.

Tavi joined halfway through because he heard his name.

"I don't find your things."

Cala said, "You found the red string."

"That is everybody string."

"You moved a chair."

"Because it was high."

"That is why we are talking."

He looked at chest.

"What's in there?"

"Things you don't touch," I said.

"I know."

"Do you?"

He looked offended.

"I didn't touch them."

Again.

True.

Important.

I was turning a near-miss into an accusation he had not earned.

"You're right," I said. "You didn't."

His face changed immediately.

Not smug.

Just less defensive.

"But you could have fallen near them, and I don't know what happens if they fall. That's my problem, not yours."

He considered.

"Can I move chairs?"

"For sitting."

"What if I need something high?"

"Ask."

"What if nobody is there?"

"Wait."

He hated this answer.

I could see him trying to build alternatives.

"What if it is important?"

"Then call someone."

"What if it is really important and nobody hears?"

"Then don't build a tower."

He frowned.

"I wasn't building a tower."

"Future policy."

Cala said, "No furniture towers."

Tavi sighed with the exhaustion of a child living under tyranny.

"Fine."

The key ended up on Cala's key ring.

Not because she became artifact custodian.

Because she already had a key ring and I did not.

I could ask for it.

That created friction.

Good friction.

An object that required the chest required another person if I wanted access while home.

I did not love this.

Which meant it was probably safer.

"What if I need something when you're out?" I asked.

Cala looked at me.

"Plan."

Disgusting.

I had expected artifact constraints like sunlight, blood, mana cost, temperature, activation words.

Instead I got Cala.

Possibly stronger.

By afternoon my room looked normal again.

That was the strangest part.

Shelf had clothes and ordinary things.

No bad lantern.

No wrapped holdfast.

No industrial stone box.

The artifacts still existed.

Just not in my visual field.

This changed my brain more than expected.

When they sat on shelves, each one produced a little unfinished thought whenever I looked up.

Holdfast.

Fixture.

Rest stone.

Safe system.

Shade unit.

Rated lamp.

Now the room contained a wall.

A shirt.

My bag.

Nothing asking to be solved.

I sat on bed.

This was suspiciously peaceful.

Lyssa came in later for her folded dark cloth.

She looked at empty shelf.

"Better."

"I hate it."

"Why?"

"I can't see them."

"That's how storage works."

"What if I forget what I own?"

She stared at me.

"Greg."

"Yes."

"You have four weird things."

"Three in chest. Oval in bag."

"You are not going to forget."

"Collections expand."

There.

She set the cloth under her arm.

"Are you planning to buy more?"

I paused too long.

"That was not a difficult question."

"I am planning to encounter more."

"That is not what I asked."

"I would like the freedom to make good decisions."

Lyssa took a breath.

Not angry.

Longer than her usual quick jabs.

"Okay. Then this is the part I mean. I don't care that you like these things. I care when the plan becomes 'I found something interesting, therefore the house has to absorb it.' Yesterday it was five copper. Today it was shelves and Tavi and a chest we were using for papers. The next one could be bigger, or hot, or loud, or something you can't carry with your crutches. You can't keep deciding only whether the object itself is worth the price. You have to decide whether you have somewhere for the whole problem it brings with it."

I listened.

Because she was right.

Annoyingly complete.

Also because she had not said I should stop.

That mattered.

"Whole problem," I repeated.

"Yes."

"Purchase price plus storage, transport, repair, compatible equipment, people in the house."

"And whether Cala wants old magic under her winter blankets."

"There are no winter blankets in that chest now."

"You know what I mean."

"Yes."

She looked at me.

"I'm not telling you not to buy things."

"I know."

"Good. Because I don't want this to become you telling people I told you not to buy things."

"I would never weaponize household nuance."

"You absolutely would."

Fair.

She left.

I sat with the phrase whole problem.

Did not write it immediately.

That would make it sound like Lyssa had delivered wisdom for my notebook instead of having an opinion about living in the same house as my increasingly stupid collection.

Later, though, when I updated the narrow scrap, I wrote:

ARTIFACT STORAGE: CEDAR CHEST / LOCKED / BACK ROOM / BLOCKED OFF DAMP WALL

CALA HAS KEY

OVAL STAYS WITH BAG

NO ACTIVE OBJECTS IN CHEST

PAPERS ABOVE OBJECTS / SEPARATE

Then:

SHADE UNIT STILL NEEDS RATED LIGHT CONTEXT

And below that:

3 C CASH / 26 C VALE

I stopped.

Then added one more line.

NEXT OBJECT COSTS MORE THAN PRICE.

That was close enough to Lyssa's point without stealing her sentence.

I looked at it.

Not a rule against buying.

A rule against pretending ownership ended at payment.

Good.

Downstairs, Tavi had finally made the spoon carrier roll three handspans before one button came off.

He shouted for everyone to look.

We looked.

The spoon tipped sideways.

The red string held.

Tavi threw both hands up.

"It worked!"

"Partly," I said.

He turned on me.

"No. It worked. The wheel fell after. That's different."

I paused.

Then nodded.

"Fair."

He grinned.

Useful distinction.

I did not write that one down.

Not everything in the house belonged to my system.

# CHAPTER 355

## THE BORROWED LAMP

The lamp found me before I found the lamp.

This was convenient enough that I distrusted it immediately.

Two days after we locked three questionable decisions in a cedar chest, Nessa knocked on the front door carrying a wooden case under one arm.

Not a small case.

Long enough that she had to turn sideways through the doorway.

Cala looked at the case.

Then at me.

"No."

"I haven't done anything."

"You are standing near it."

"That standard is impossible."

Nessa said, "Good morning."

Cala pointed at the table.

"Put it there."

Nessa did.

The case landed with the careful weight of something expensive enough to have rules.

I knew that kind of weight.

"What is it?" I asked.

"A lamp."

I looked at the case.

"House-left aisle," Nessa said.

"Why is that here?"

Lyssa came in from the back room carrying folded cloth.

Saw Nessa.

Saw case.

Saw me.

"Absolutely not."

"Again, I have done nothing."

"You're interested."

"That is not an offense."

"It becomes one around equipment."

Nessa opened the case.

Inside lay a narrow stage lamp.

Not one of the big front housings.

Portable.

Brass spine.

Flat round base.

White ceramic light cup protected by three dark ribs.

A small core housing at rear.

Two sliding shutters on either side.

And, stamped into the inside of the case lid, a maker-service plate.

I read it before I meant to.

LANTERN WORKS / HOUSE SERVICE 4

RATED TABLE / WALL / CARRIED LAMP

"Why is that here?"

Nessa pulled a folded note from coat pocket.

"Because Jori wants the house-left aisle lamp checked before tomorrow night, and because I told him you bought something stupid."

I looked at Lyssa.

Lyssa looked innocent.

Too innocent.

"How did you know?"

Nessa said, "Lyssa told Hara."

Lyssa said, "Hara came for the gray cloth."

"And you told her I spent five copper on a bad lantern."

"I told her you spent five copper on something that does not make light."

"That phrasing is hostile."

"It is accurate."

Nessa continued, "Hara told Jori because she thought it was funny. Jori stopped thinking it was funny when she said Three Reeds."

There.

Not coincidence.

Social residue.

People carried versions of me around when I wasn't present.

Apparently they also carried my bad purchases.

"What did Jori say?"

Nessa unfolded note.

"He said, and I quote, 'If the idiot actually has an old Three Reeds shade, do not let him put it on anything until I see the card.'"

"He called me idiot?"

"I may have improved punctuation."

"That was not punctuation."

"He wants the card."

I looked at the stage lamp.

Then the service plate.

Rated table, wall, carried lamp.

The family card upstairs said:

FOR TABLE / WALL / CARRIED SERVICE.

PAIR ONLY WITH RATED LAMP ASSEMBLY.

Close enough to become interesting.

Not close enough to become safe.

"Is this lamp rated for Three Reeds?"

"No idea," Nessa said.

Good.

"Jori?"

"Also no idea. That's why he wants the card and the unit before anyone connects anything."

Better.

"Why bring lamp here?"

"I didn't. I was taking it to service court. I stopped here because your house is on the way and because Rinna wants the aisle lamp back by second bell tomorrow."

That made the world less convenient.

The lamp had not arrived for me.

I had merely become a side errand attached to an existing equipment route.

Excellent.

"Where is service court?"

"Theatre rear yard. Jori has the bench open until midday."

I looked toward the back room.

Cala's key ring hung from her belt.

New friction.

I liked it less when it worked.

"Can I bring the shade unit?"

Nessa shrugged.

"That's the request."

"Test it?"

"That's not the request."

Right.

I looked at Cala.

"Key?"

She studied me.

Not suspicious.

Checking whether I had actually planned.

"Where?"

"Theatre rear service court. Jori. Nessa. Existing rated lamp. No home activation."

"Back when?"

"Before midday if he has time."

Nessa said, "I am leaving in about one minute whether he comes or not."

Cala handed me the key.

"Bring it back."

"The key?"

"All of it."

Fair.

I opened the cedar chest.

The shade unit sat where I had left it.

Inactive.

Upright.

Family card above.

The holdfast remained wrapped.

Rest stone remained boxed.

I took only the shade unit and card.

Closed chest.

Locked it.

Returned key to Cala before leaving.

That was satisfying in a way I refused to examine.

The shade unit went into shoulder bag wrapped loosely, handle out.

Nessa watched.

"Five copper?"

"Yes."

"For that?"

"You work in theatre."

"That is not a defense."

"You own painted walls."

"The theatre owns painted walls."

"Collectively worse."

She considered.

"Fair."

We left.

The rear service court smelled like warm wood, dust, lamp oil that was not actually lamp oil anymore, and somebody's lunch.

Jori had claimed one long bench under the awning.

The stage lamp case sat open at one end by the time we arrived.

Another lamp housing was clamped upright farther down.

Jori wore the same leather-and-metal support at his left wrist. It clicked once when he tightened a small screw.

He looked up.

Looked at me.

Looked at bag handle.

"You actually bought it."

"That sounds judgmental."

"It is diagnostic."

Nessa put the borrowed lamp case beside him.

"House left. Rinna wants it tomorrow."

"I know."

She left without ceremony.

No audience.

No event.

Just work.

Jori held out hand.

"Card first."

I gave him card.

He read slowly.

Not because reading was difficult.

Because equipment language mattered.

He turned it over.

Blank back.

"General family card," he said.

"Yes."

"No model line."

"Yes."

"No rated pair list."

"Water damage maybe."

"Maybe. Or this card never had one."

He handed it back.

"Show me unit. Inactive."

I did.

Set it on clear section of bench.

Jori did not touch rear stud.

He turned unit by base.

Looked underneath.

Three Reeds mark.

Looked at vents.

Used a thin wood strip to check that each opening was physically clear.

Not inside.

Just mouth.

"Core warm now?"

"No."

He put two fingers near rear plate without touching stone.

Then inspected front shutter.

"You moved this?"

"Seller did. Open, half, closed. No visible light."

"Because it isn't a lamp."

"I know."

"Do you?"

"Card says shade unit."

"Card says family shade unit. Your object has glass."

"Later combined housings sometimes did."

He looked at me.

There it was.

Too much knowledge.

Not impossible.

Just enough to require source.

"Where'd you hear that?"

"Old equipment stories."

"From who?"

I shrugged.

"People."

He stared another second.

Then let it go.

People was a terrible source.

Fortunately most of civilization used it.

"This theatre lamp is current-rated carried service," he said. "That does not mean rated pair."

"Agreed."

"Connector?"

He turned shade unit sideways.

There was no obvious connector.

I had noticed.

He found something I had not.

Under the rear handle mount, hidden by black cord wrap, was a shallow brass tongue with two slots.

Not electrical.

Mechanical mounting interface.

He did not unwrap cord.

Just moved it enough to expose edge.

Then checked theatre lamp case.

Inside lid, beneath service plate, two spare mounting brackets sat in narrow grooves.

One was a flat U.

One was a bent double hook.

Jori held double hook near Three Reeds tongue.

Did not insert.

The spacing looked close.

Very close.

"Compatible?" I asked.

"Spacing compatible."

Good correction.

"Rated?"

"No."

"Safe?"

"Not established."

"Can we establish it?"

"Maybe."

He looked at card again.

Then lamp plate.

"The problem is pair only with rated lamp assembly. This lamp is rated. Your shade unit is from a family that used carried service. Bracket spacing matches one service bracket. None of that proves the pair."

"What would?"

"Exact model paper. Pair table. Service ledger. Or controlled low-output trial with enough distance and no idiots."

"How many idiots allowed?"

"One."

"Which one?"

"I haven't decided."

He checked time by light through awning.

"I need house-left lamp checked anyway. Low output is part of that check. We can observe your unit beside it first. No mounting. No connection. If nothing strange happens, maybe bracketed position with lamp at minimum. If I don't like it, we stop."

"Cost?"

He looked at me.

"For me doing my scheduled lamp check while your object sits nearby? Nothing. If we start adapting hardware, opening housings, or doing work for you, then money."

Fair.

Very fair.

"And if it damages theatre lamp?"

"Then we don't do the part likely to damage theatre lamp."

I liked Jori.

Not emotionally.

Procedurally.

He moved us to the dark test passage behind the service court.

Not fully dark.

A narrow stone corridor with one shuttered opening at far end.

The theatre used it to check portable lights without blacking out a room.

Useful infrastructure existed because other people had jobs before I arrived.

Jori set the house-left lamp on a marked waist-high stand.

Shade unit stayed on a separate wood block two feet to its left.

No contact.

"Your unit off," he said.

"Yes."

He activated theatre lamp at minimum.

Soft white light filled near corridor.

Not bright.

Enough to see floor joints and far shutter.

Jori slid side shutters until light formed a broad forward wedge.

Spill still washed both walls.

"Normal minimum," he said.

He marked two charcoal lines on wall where obvious edge faded.

Then nodded at my unit.

"Activate. Don't touch shutter after."

I pressed rear stud.

Cloudy stone warmed under plate.

No light came from shade unit.

For two breaths, nothing obvious happened.

Then the left wall changed.

Not dark.

That would have been dramatic.

The wash from theatre lamp narrowed.

The brightest part stayed almost the same.

But the soft useless light beyond left edge thinned.

Jori leaned forward.

I did not move.

The right wall looked nearly unchanged.

Left wall, nearest shade unit face, lost a band of spill perhaps hand-width at first charcoal line.

Not vanished.

Reduced.

Enough that old stone texture became less obvious.

"Off," Jori said.

I pressed stud.

Within a breath or two, spill returned.

Jori made new mark.

"Again."

On.

Same direction.

Same broad reduction.

Not exactly same boundary.

Close.

"Off."

Off.

He touched shade-unit rear plate carefully with back of two fingers.

"Warm."

"Seller demo did that."

"How long was it active?"

"Short."

"Keep it short."

We waited until plate cooled.

Then he rotated shade unit ninety degrees on its own block.

No mounting.

No lamp movement.

"Again."

On.

This time the far end of corridor changed.

Subtly.

The lamp's main forward wedge remained.

But beyond a line angled toward shutter, the diffuse glow around it tightened.

Not enough to hide the lamp.

Not enough to make corridor dark.

Enough to make its light less socially expansive.

That was the phrase my brain produced.

Light with worse manners.

No.

Better manners.

It stayed where told.

Mostly.

Jori said, "Off."

I turned it off.

He looked at unit.

Then card.

Then corridor.

"Well."

That was all.

I smiled.

"Don't."

"I'm not doing anything."

"You're doing victory face."

"It worked."

"It produced a repeatable visible effect twice in one lamp context."

"That is longer for worked."

"Longer is safer."

Tavi would have hated him.

Or loved him.

Possibly both.

"Can we try shutter positions?"

"Not yet."

"Why?"

"Because we already changed orientation. That's one variable enough."

"You changed orientation."

"And now I'm refusing your second variable."

Fine.

"Bracket?"

"Not today."

"You said maybe."

"I saw enough."

"Enough for what?"

"Enough to know your five copper was not entirely stupid."

High praise.

He took the theatre lamp back to service court.

Continued its actual check.

My shade unit sat inactive on wood block cooling.

I watched him inspect lamp shutters, base, core seat, and one loose side screw.

The world had not stopped because my artifact worked.

That was good.

After several minutes he said, without looking up, "If you use that near somebody else's light, ask first."

"Obviously."

He looked at me.

"You bought it before you had a lamp."

"Fair."

"It changes spill. That can change sightlines. Warnings. Signals. Who can see what. Don't treat reduced stray light like harmless darkness."

That was better than a safety lecture because it was true in several directions.

"And heat?"

"Rear plate warmed fast. Maybe normal. Maybe old core. You don't know duration. Short activations until you know more. Vents open. No wrapping active."

"Agreed."

"No mounting to this lamp until pair rating is established or I decide a controlled bracket trial is worth risk."

"Agreed."

He finally looked at me.

"You agree too easily when you get what you wanted."

"I am pleasant in victory."

"You're unbearable in victory."

"Still victory."

He returned the family card.

"Write down what you actually saw."

"I do that."

"I know. That's why I said it."

Interesting.

Another version of me carried around when absent.

I packed shade unit inactive after it cooled.

No fee.

No theatre job.

No promise of future testing.

Jori's work had existed without me.

I had simply arrived with an object that fit one useful question at the edge of his day.

On the way home, I kept thinking about the corridor.

Not because effect was enormous.

It wasn't.

That was why I liked it.

A lamp still lit what it lit.

The shade unit changed the waste around that light.

The announcement.

The glow around corners.

The soft evidence that somebody nearby had decided to see.

At nineteen, with three copper and twenty-six owed, I owned an object that could make light less talkative.

That was useful.

Specific.

And dangerous in ways that were not immediately about explosions.

Better.

At home Cala asked for report before key.

"It works."

She held out hand.

"Longer."

Jori had infected the household.

"In one controlled context, beside a current-rated theatre lamp at minimum output, unmounted and physically separate, it repeatedly reduced visible stray light beyond one side of its face. Rotating the unit changed where the reduction appeared."

Cala gave me the key.

"Better."

Lyssa was at table sewing.

"Five copper?"

"Less stupid."

"How much less?"

"Enough."

"That is not a number."

"Neither is joy."

"You have three copper."

"Joy is economically independent."

She snorted.

I put shade unit back in cedar chest.

Inactive.

Cool.

Upright.

Card above it.

Locked chest.

Returned key.

Then wrote:

THREE REEDS / CONTROLLED THEATRE LAMP CONTEXT

CURRENT-RATED CARRIED LAMP / MIN OUTPUT

NO MOUNT / NO CONTACT

UNIT ON -> REPEATABLE REDUCTION OF STRAY LIGHT BEYOND ONE FACE

MAIN LIT AREA MOSTLY PRESERVED IN OBSERVED TEST

ROTATE UNIT -> REDUCTION MOVES WITH ORIENTATION

NOT DARKNESS

NOT CONCEALMENT

NOT FULL LIGHT REMOVAL

REAR PLATE WARMS QUICKLY

SHORT USE ONLY FOR NOW

NO BRACKET TEST

NO SHUTTER-POSITION TEST

ASK BEFORE USING NEAR OTHER PEOPLE'S LIGHT

Then:

3 C CASH

26 C VALE

I stared at the page.

Five copper.

A real effect.

No new money.

No new job.

No secret fortune.

Just one old object that changed a small rule of the world around me.

I added one final line.

LIGHT CAN BE QUIETER.

That sounded stupid.

I left it.

# CHAPTER 356

## THE BLUE HOUR

The fog came in speaking with other people's voices.

This was less dramatic than it sounds.

Carrow had procedures.

Most frightening things became tolerable once somebody painted instructions on a board.

I was with Lyssa when the first bell changed.

Not rang.

Changed.

The afternoon street bell at East Cloth normally gave one clean bronze note before the late-market shift.

This one struck and answered itself from half a street away.

Same pitch.

Same bell.

Wrong direction.

Lyssa stopped.

So did three other people nearby.

A woman carrying folded baskets looked toward an alley.

The actual bell tower stood behind us.

"Blue hour," she said.

Then kept walking.

I looked at Lyssa.

"Is that local terminology or are we about to become weather?"

"You don't know?"

"I know echo fog. I don't know what Carrow calls everything because Carrow enjoys branding."

That was true.

Old life had included several kinds of mana-heavy fog that bent sound.

Usually near rivers.

Sometimes old battlefields.

One terrible marsh outside Vellen where every frog sounded like it was sitting inside your left ear.

That one had not been dangerous.

Just persuasive.

This fog was already visible at the end of the street.

Not gray.

Blue.

Very faint blue.

More like daylight had forgotten how to leave properly.

It rolled low first.

Ankles.

Cart wheels.

Thresholds.

Then climbed.

Shopkeepers reacted before anyone panicked.

One pulled a bright yellow rope across the mouth of a side lane.

Another turned a hanging sign sideways.

A boy in a red vest started walking down the street ringing a small handbell twice, pausing, twice again.

Unlike the tower bell, his bell did not seem to duplicate.

Interesting.

I did not ask why.

Growth.

Lyssa grabbed my sleeve.

"Marra's first."

"We were already going to Marra's."

"I'm reminding you before you decide to study weather."

"I was observing public procedure."

"With your whole face."

Again.

My face needed regulation.

We had been heading to Marra's green door because Lyssa had cloth to return and a message to deliver.

Not my cloth.

Not my message.

I was there because we had left the house at the same time and because walking with somebody was sometimes simply walking with somebody.

This concept remained underdeveloped in my professional life.

The blue fog reached us at knee height.

Cold.

Not freezing.

Wet without making cloth wet immediately.

Then someone said my name behind me.

"Greg."

I turned.

Nobody.

Lyssa did not.

"Don't."

"That sounded like Cala."

"Cala is home."

"I know."

"Then don't."

Reasonable.

The same voice came from ahead now.

"Greg."

Not exactly Cala.

Close enough to catch attention.

Wrong enough once I listened.

Echo fog did not create voices from nothing.

Usually.

It carried existing sound along mana gradients and returned it late, bent, or displaced.

A name spoken somewhere could arrive from somewhere else.

A laugh could move streets.

Sometimes your own steps followed you.

The danger was not supernatural deception.

The danger was human reflex.

We followed voices.

Especially familiar ones.

The red-vest boy kept his paired handbell rhythm.

Two.

Pause.

Two.

People started favoring main streets.

Side lanes emptied.

A cart driver stopped before turning and physically checked both directions rather than calling around the corner.

A woman with two children tied a strip of cloth from her wrist to the older child's wrist.

Local adaptation.

Good.

"Do we need rope?" I asked.

Lyssa looked at me.

"I am not tying myself to you."

"You say that now."

"I will say it in worse weather too."

We kept walking.

The fog rose to waist height.

Then chest.

Visibility remained decent.

Thirty feet, maybe more.

Buildings softened.

People became darker shapes before becoming people again.

Sound got stupid.

A vendor shouted, "Apples!"

The word came from the roof.

A baby cried from somewhere under the street.

Someone laughed directly beside my right shoulder.

There was nobody there.

I did not turn.

Progress.

Lyssa's hand remained on my sleeve.

Not gripping tightly.

Contact.

Useful.

"You do this often?" I asked.

"Once or twice most wet seasons. Sometimes none."

"Bad?"

"Depends."

"Useful local answer."

"If it stays light blue, mostly annoying. If it goes dark blue, people close more streets."

"Why?"

"I don't know."

Good.

She was allowed not to know things about the city she lived in.

People used systems without studying them.

I knew this.

I was trying to become one of them occasionally.

At the next crossing, a city worker had already hung a white board from a post.

BLUE HOUR

MAIN ROUTES OPEN

SIDE CUTS CLOSED WHERE ROPED

DO NOT FOLLOW VOICES INTO CLOSED LANES

VISUAL CONFIRM BEFORE CROSSING CART ROUTES

CHILDREN / CONFUSED PERSONS TO MARKED DOORS

Below that, someone had added in chalk:

IF YOU HEAR YOURSELF, IGNORE HIM

I liked Carrow.

"Marked doors?" I asked.

Lyssa pointed.

Several shops had blue-white squares hung outside.

Temporary refuge points.

Not shelters from fog itself.

Places for children, lost people, frightened people, maybe drunk people, to wait until someone came.

A bakery had one.

So did a cooper.

A small shrine door farther down.

Distributed city competence again.

No grand emergency center required.

Then Lyssa's voice said, "Greg, left," from behind me.

At the same time the actual Lyssa tugged my sleeve right.

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

"That was rude," I said.

"What?"

"You told me left from behind."

"I did not."

"I know."

Her expression changed slightly.

Not fear.

Attention.

The fog had climbed to face height.

Still pale blue.

Still visually manageable.

But voices were becoming more convincing because they were closer to current speech.

The echo had less delay now.

Maybe.

I was not timing it.

Not my job.

We reached Marra's street.

Rope across the shortest cut.

Closed.

Lyssa stopped.

"Long way."

"Yes."

She did not complain.

The green door was only two streets away by normal route.

Now four.

This was why magic mattered even when nobody cast spells at anyone.

A weather event could rearrange a city.

We took the long way.

Halfway around, somebody shouted Lyssa's name.

"Lyssa!"

She turned.

I caught her elbow.

She froze.

Not because I had authority.

Because she realized at the same moment.

The voice came again from a closed alley.

"Lyssa! Wait!"

A woman's voice.

Could have been Marra.

Could have been Jessa.

Could have been anyone who had said Lyssa's name within the wrong patch of fog.

Lyssa stared toward the yellow rope.

Then at me.

"That sounded like Jessa."

"Do you see Jessa?"

"No."

"Then not enough."

She exhaled.

"I know."

Important.

No lecture.

No Greg saves competent woman from weather.

She knew.

Her body had moved before the rest of her did.

Mine had done the same with Cala's voice.

We kept walking.

Then the problem got more ordinary.

We lost contact.

Not dramatically.

No scream.

No magical wall.

A handcart came through the crossing.

People shifted.

I moved to the edge to give wheels room.

Lyssa stepped around a woman carrying a basket.

Someone between us stopped suddenly because a voice called their name from a doorway.

Three bodies changed lanes.

The fog swallowed half a step.

And Lyssa was gone.

Not gone.

I could see six people.

Then eight.

Then a tall woman in dark cloth who was not Lyssa.

"Lyssa?"

Mistake.

Her name returned from two directions.

"Lyssa?"

My own voice, displaced.

Excellent.

I shut up.

The paired handbell sounded somewhere ahead.

Two.

Pause.

Two.

Main route.

Stay put or move to marked door.

Those were likely local rules.

I looked for blue-white square.

Cooper's doorway twenty feet away.

I moved there.

Not because I thought Lyssa would magically know.

Because if both of us used public procedure, we had a chance of behaving predictably.

The cooper had become a temporary collection of people who were not in crisis but had lost somebody within thirty seconds.

An old man.

Two girls maybe ten and twelve.

A young delivery worker holding three wrapped parcels.

The cooper himself stood at door with a chalk board.

"Name?" he asked me.

"Greg. Looking for Lyssa. Tall. Dark skin. Dark cloth bundle."

He wrote:

GREG / LYSSA

"Stay here unless you see her."

"Good."

One of the girls said, "My brother sounds like he's in the wall."

The other said, "He always sounds like that."

Sibling support.

I leaned on my crutches and waited.

This was difficult.

I wanted to move.

Of course I wanted to move.

Moving felt like doing.

Doing felt safer than waiting.

This instinct had caused several problems across two lives.

I stayed.

A minute passed.

Maybe two.

Then Lyssa's voice said, very close, "Greg?"

I did not answer.

The cooper looked at me.

"You waiting for Lyssa?"

"Yes."

"That sounded near."

"Yes."

"You going?"

"No."

"Good."

Professional validation.

Then someone touched my shoulder.

I almost hit them with a crutch.

Not because I am violent.

Because I am experienced.

I stopped before doing it.

Lyssa stood behind me.

Actual Lyssa.

Visible.

Annoyed.

"Why didn't you answer?"

"Because your voice had already been lying to me."

"I was standing right there."

"Now you are."

She looked at cooper board.

Saw our names.

"You went to a marked door."

"I followed procedure."

"You?"

"I am capable of growth under supervision."

The cooper said, "Take reunion elsewhere."

Reasonable.

We moved aside.

Lyssa explained she had done almost the same thing.

When she lost me, she went to the nearest marked door on the other side of crossing.

They had a runner going between marked doors every few minutes carrying written names.

Her door had sent:

LYSSA LOOKING FOR GREG / CRUTCHES

My cooper had received it before she walked over.

I had not noticed because the runner came from behind me.

Again.

Distributed procedure.

No magic solution.

Just writing.

"Crutches," I said.

"Useful description."

"Tall and devastating would have worked."

"Too many men would answer."

"Cruel."

She touched my sleeve again.

This time deliberately.

"New rule."

"Which?"

"If we lose each other, marked door."

"Nearest?"

"Nearest open marked door. Don't chase voices."

"And if no marked door?"

She thought.

"Last place we both saw each other."

"Good."

"And don't shout my name unless you can see me."

"Also good."

"Because then your stupid voice multiplies."

"My voice is excellent."

"There were four of you."

"Nightmare."

"Exactly."

That became shorthand.

Not romantic.

Not symbolic.

Useful.

We finally reached Marra's green door.

It was marked blue-white.

Of course it was.

Jessa opened it before Lyssa knocked twice.

"You took forever."

Lyssa said, "Fog."

Jessa said, "Yes, I have windows."

Different speech register.

Good.

Marra was inside with two customers and a boy I did not know sitting near wall because he had gotten separated from his aunt.

The boy held a wooden token with an address scratched into it.

Marra had already sent someone to the nearest marked door with his name.

No one made a speech.

Lyssa delivered cloth.

Delivered message.

Marra answered with work shorthand I did not understand.

Lyssa understood.
