I stayed out of it.

Progress continued irresponsibly.

The fog deepened outside while we were there.

Not dark blue.

Still pale.

But enough that Marra said, "Wait for second clearing bell."

"How long?" I asked.

"Could be ten minutes. Could be an hour."

"Excellent scheduling."

She looked at me.

"You can leave."

"I enjoy waiting."

"Nobody believes that."

We waited.

The separated boy's aunt arrived first.

She came in angry enough to survive any weather.

"You left the spice stall."

The boy said, "You told me go to marked door if I lost you."

She stopped.

Closed her mouth.

Then hugged him.

"Yes. Good. Still angry."

Excellent parenting.

Ten minutes later, the city bell struck once.

Then struck again from three different streets.

Nobody moved.

Marra said, "Not clearing."

Good.

Five minutes later, paired handbells started outside.

Then a different bell pattern.

Three slow notes.

One pause.

Three slow notes.

Jessa opened door and looked at street.

A red-vest runner shouted while remaining visible:

"MAIN ROUTES CLEARING. SIDE CUTS STAY CLOSED UNTIL WHITE ROPES."

His voice returned from somewhere above us after he finished.

Nobody followed the echo.

We left by main route.

The blue fog was thinning from the top down.

Faces returned before feet.

Street sounds still arrived wrong, but less convincingly.

At one crossing, I heard myself say "Greg" from an alley.

I ignored him.

He had terrible judgment.

By the time we reached home, the fog had dropped back to knee height.

Cala opened door.

"You took forever."

"Fog."

"I have windows."

I looked at Lyssa.

Lyssa looked at me.

Apparently this was a local sentence.

Tavi ran in from the other room.

"Did you hear yourself?"

"Yes."

"What did you say?"

"Mostly my name."

"Did you answer?"

"Once."

He looked disappointed.

"You're not supposed to."

"I learned."

"I knew already."

"Congratulations."

He began explaining blue-hour rules in the way children explain weather they have survived twice.

"If it sounds like somebody behind you but they're not behind you then you don't go behind you and if you get lost you go to the blue-white door but not the door if the square is covered because that means full and you don't follow dogs because dogs can hear the wrong thing too and last year Kalo followed his own whistle all the way to the fish street and his mom got so mad she made him hold her bag for like a week."

I believed approximately half of this.

Possibly all.

Cala asked, "You two stayed together?"

Lyssa said, "No."

I said, "Briefly."

Cala looked at us.

Lyssa explained marked doors.

No drama.

No blame.

Then she said, "We have a rule now."

"Marked door," I said.

"Nearest open marked door," she corrected.

"And no shouting names unless visible."

Cala nodded.

"Good."

That was all.

No artifact came out.

No money moved.

No job appeared.

No one asked me to solve the fog.

The city had managed before I arrived and would continue after I died.

This was comforting.

Upstairs, I wrote nothing about mana density.

Nothing about echo delay.

Nothing about why paired handbells seemed easier to localize.

Those belonged to people who actually studied blue hour.

I wrote only:

BLUE HOUR

DO NOT FOLLOW FAMILIAR VOICE WITHOUT VISUAL CONFIRM

IF SEPARATED: NEAREST OPEN MARKED DOOR

NO SHOUTING NAMES UNLESS VISIBLE

Then:

LYSSA KNOWS SAME RULE

I stared at that.

Too formal.

Crossed it out.

Wrote:

MARKED DOOR.

That was enough.

Some relationships accumulated through declarations.

Some through fights.

Some through promises.

Ours had apparently acquired emergency weather shorthand.

I liked that better.

# CHAPTER 357

## THE GOOD COAT

Lyssa wanted a coat.

This became my problem because I made the mistake of having an opinion.

"That one," I said.

She looked at the coat.

Then at me.

"No."

"You haven't touched it."

"I can see it."

"That's generally how coats begin."

"It looks like a clerk."

The coat did look like a clerk.

Dark brown wool.

Clean collar.

Straight front.

Four plain buttons.

No decoration except a narrow stitched line at the cuffs.

Respectable.

Warm.

Probably durable.

Deeply uninteresting.

"Clerks are dry," I said.

"Clerks are boring."

"Those properties can coexist."

The seller, an older man with a measuring cord around his neck, said, "That one is six copper."

Lyssa looked at me.

"See?"

"I don't understand what price has to do with clerks."

"Six copper to look boring."

"You were about to spend five on the green one."

"The green one is nice."

The green one was nice.

Dark green with a fitted waist, angled pockets, brass-colored fasteners, and a shoulder line that made Lyssa look like she intended to collect debts personally.

It was also thinner.

The seller had already told us that.

"Lined through body," he said, touching the brown coat. "Half-lined sleeve. Green is fashion weight. Good for dry cold. Not long rain."

Lyssa said, "I don't need long rain."

I looked outside.

A gutter still dripped from yesterday.

"Carrow appears to disagree."

"I have survived rain before."

"Without a coat."

"Exactly."

There was no winning against evidence used badly.

I leaned on both crutches and considered leaving.

I had not come to buy anything.

Important distinction.

This was Lyssa's purchase.

Her money.

Her work.

Her body.

Her coat.

I was there because she had asked if I wanted to walk with her after breakfast, and because apparently the previous day's blue-hour separation had not convinced either of us that walking together was dangerous.

The fog was gone.

Carrow had returned to ordinary gray weather and ordinary people making expensive decisions about cloth.

Lyssa lifted the green coat from hook.

"Try it," I said.

"I was going to."

"Then why are you arguing with me?"

"Because you started helping."

Fair.

The seller had a curtained fitting corner.

Lyssa disappeared behind it.

I stayed outside and became furniture.

The shop was narrow.

Coats along one wall.

Work jackets along other.

Hooks with capes, rain shells, heavy aprons.

A small rack near counter held secondhand gloves.

I looked at none of it.

This required effort.

There was an old leather shoulder guard with a metal hinge near the gloves.

I did not look at that either.

Growth.

"Greg."

I turned.

Lyssa came out wearing green coat.

It looked good.

Very good.

Which was inconvenient because I had been correct about brown.

The green fit her shoulders closely.

Waist pulled slightly when she reached forward.

She raised both arms.

Fabric tightened across back.

Seller saw it too.

"One size up."

"Then it'll be loose here," Lyssa said, touching waist.

"Can take waist."

"How much?"

"One copper alteration."

"No."

Immediate.

Not poor.

Preference.

I liked that.

She moved arms again.

"I need to carry."

Seller nodded.

"Then not this cut."

Also good.

No pressure.

No pretending fashion coat was work coat.

Lyssa took it off.

"Still nice," she said.

"Very."

"You sound happy it's wrong."

"I am never happy when reality agrees with me."

"Liar."

"Constantly delighted."

The brown clerk coat came next.

She put it on.

Looked at mirror.

Made face.

"No."

"Move first."

"I can move while ugly."

"Excellent. Test conditions."

She raised arms.

Reached forward.

Crossed them.

Turned.

Bent.

Pockets stayed accessible.

Shoulders did not bind.

Seller pulled lightly at back hem.

"Enough room for vest under."

Lyssa froze.

Not visibly to seller.

To me, yes.

Vest.

Her load vest.

The unresolved one.

Not my project.

I said nothing.

She looked at mirror again.

Brown coat still looked like a clerk.

A competent clerk now.

Possibly one who could lift boxes.

"How rainproof?" she asked.

"Not proof. Treated wool. Sheds ordinary rain. Soaks in long rain. Dry before storing."

"Repair?"

"Any cloth shop can do seams. Treatment can be renewed."

"Buttons?"

"Standard."

"Six?"

"Six."

She took it off.

"Five."

Seller shook head.

"Six."

"Five and I don't make you pretend the cuff stitching is decorative."

The seller looked offended.

"It is decorative."

"It is reinforcing the fold."

"It can be two things."

I liked him.

Lyssa said, "Five and one bit."

"Six."

"You haven't moved."

"Neither have you."

She looked at me.

I looked at ceiling.

Not my negotiation.

This was harder than artifact appraisal.

At least artifacts did not ask me whether brown made them look like clerks.

Lyssa put brown coat back on.

Looked at mirror.

Then green coat.

Then brown.

"I hate this one."

Seller said, "Then don't buy it."

Perfect.

She laughed.

Actually laughed.

"You are terrible at selling."

"I sell coats. I don't sell liking coats."

She took brown off again.

"Do you have something between these?"

"Between useful and pretty?"

"Yes."

"Expensive."

"Of course."

He went to back.

Returned with charcoal coat.

Longer than green.

Shorter than brown.

Soft black-gray wool.

Simple front.

Slight shape at waist without pulling tight.

Deep angled pockets.

Dark horn buttons.

No bright metal.

The collar could fold flat or button high.

Lyssa touched it.

Her face changed.

There.

Want.

I knew that face now.

Not because she made it often.

Because I had recently made my own version over a bad lantern.

"How much?" she asked.

"Nine."

She let go.

Immediate.

Interesting.

Seller did not chase.

"Why nine?" I asked.

Lyssa looked at me sharply.

I held up hand.

"Information. Not buying."

Seller answered anyway.

"Denser wool. Better shoulder cut. Double pocket facing. High collar. Treatment included."

"Alteration?"

"Minor waist or sleeve, included if done now."

Lyssa said, "No."

Seller nodded.

Hung it back.

And that should have been end.

We left.

We made it half a street.

Lyssa was quiet.

I did not ask.

This was deliberate.

One street.

Two.

At third, she said, "Nine is stupid."

"For a coat?"

"For me."

Different sentence.

I looked at her.

She kept walking.

"Why?"

"Because I don't need it."

"You need a coat."

"I need something dry. I don't need nine copper."

"Nine copper is not an object."

"You know what I mean."

I did.

We walked another few steps.

Her hands were empty.

Mine had crutches.

The street smelled like wet stone and bread.

Someone was arguing over fish farther down.

Normal day.

"You wanted it," I said.

"So?"

"Nothing."

She looked at me.

"You spent five on something you couldn't even use."

"Correct."

"And now you're going to tell me to spend nine on a coat."

"No."

"You were about to."

"I was about to ask whether nine changes anything important."

She frowned.

"What?"

"If you spend nine, what can't you do?"

"Have nine copper."

"Excellent financial analysis."

"Greg."

"Do you have something else you need the money for?"

She was quiet.

That was not my invitation to inventory her finances.

I waited.

"Maybe," she said.

"Then that's real."

"Maybe isn't real."

"It is if you're keeping room for it."

She looked at me again.

"You sound like Mara."

"Cruel."

"You like Mara."

"Procedurally."

"That isn't a way to like people."

"It is one of many."

We stopped near a bakery awning because rain began lightly.

Not blue fog.

Just rain.

Lyssa stood under awning without coat.

This improved argument materially.

"I have twelve," she said.

I did not react.

Not because twelve was huge.

Because she had chosen to tell me.

"Okay."

"Twelve copper. Some bits."

"Okay."

"Nine leaves three."

There it was.

My number.

I almost laughed.

Did not.

"Three is survivable," I said.

"For you."

"For me now. Maybe not for you."

"Exactly."

Good.

Her money had different obligations.

Different uncertainty.

Different work.

Different appetite for being stupid.

"What are you keeping room for?" I asked.

She leaned against wall.

"Materials. Maybe. If Marra's thing turns into more work. Or if I need to replace the good shears. Or if the vest needs something. Or if Cala needs house money. Or if nothing happens and I just want money."

All valid.

"Then nine is expensive."

"Yes."

"But not because you're not worth nine."

She looked at me.

I regretted sentence immediately.

Not because wrong.

Because it sounded like I had prepared it.

I had not.

"Don't," she said.

"I know."

"Don't make it a thing."

"Already stopped."

She stared at rain.

Then said, "The brown one is ugly."

"Deeply."

"Six is also a lot."

"Less than nine."

"Thank you."

"I contribute."

Rain strengthened.

We remained under awning.

A woman ran past with a basket over head.

Someone across street shouted that fish were cheaper because weather was ruining everybody's mood.

Lyssa said, "What would you do?"

Dangerous.

"With your money?"

"Yes."

"Nothing."

"Useless."

"I mean I wouldn't decide for you."

"I asked."

"Then I'd buy neither today."

She turned.

"Why?"

"Because you hate the six-copper coat and the nine-copper coat makes you nervous enough that you're trying to turn wanting it into a moral failure."

Her face went still.

I continued because survival instincts had left.

"So leave. If you still want charcoal after a few days, then you're choosing the coat, not escaping the shop. If it's gone, it's gone."

"That's easy for you to say after the load lock."

There.

She knew that story too.

Of course she did.

"Yes," I said.

"You hated losing it."

"Yes."

"And you still think walking was right."

"Yes."

She looked back at rain.

"Annoying."

"I have developed principles through suffering."

"You bought a bad lantern two days later."

"And it works."

"Partly."

"Tavi says that distinction matters."

She laughed again.

Rain eased.

We walked home without a coat.

This should not have felt like an outcome.

It did.

At home Cala looked at Lyssa's empty hands.

"Nothing?"

"Nothing."

"Didn't like them?"

Lyssa took off damp outer wrap.

"Liked one too much."

Cala nodded as if this were a complete category of problem.

Maybe it was.

"Price?"

"Nine."

Cala winced.

"Good coat?"

"Very."

"Then maybe."

Lyssa looked betrayed.

"You too?"

"I said maybe."

Tavi appeared.

"What color?"

"Charcoal."

"That's not a color."

"It is."

"That's burned wood."

"Also a color."

He considered this suspiciously.

I went upstairs.

Did not open cedar chest.

Did not look at artifacts.

I sat and took out scrap.

Almost wrote:

LYSSA / COAT / 9 C

Then stopped.

Absolutely not.

Her money was not my ledger.

I crossed out the blank line before it existed.

Instead I wrote my own:

3 C CASH

26 C VALE

Then underneath:

WANTING SOMETHING DOES NOT REQUIRE BUYING IT TODAY.

I stared at that.

It sounded annoyingly mature.

I added:

UNLESS IT IS VERY GOOD.

Better.

Downstairs Lyssa called, "Greg."

"What?"

"If I go back tomorrow, don't say anything."

I smiled.

"About the coat?"

"About anything."

"Impossible."

"Try."

"For how long?"

"The whole shop."

"That's unreasonable."

"Then don't come."

I considered.

"Can I make faces?"

A pause.

"No."

Cruel.

"Then I won't come."

"Good."

Another pause.

"Maybe come."

There it was.

Not a decision.

Not yet.

Just room for one.

# CHAPTER 358

## THE HANGING ROAD

The bridge lifted while I was on it.

Not collapsed.

That would have been simpler.

It lifted.

I had one crutch planted, one moving, right foot between them, and a basket of black-root under my left arm when the stone under me rose three inches and the entire bridge made a sound like somebody dragging a chair across the sky.

Everyone stopped.

Good instinct.

Then the bridge rose another inch.

Worse.

"Nobody move!"

That came from ahead.

A woman in a yellow work coat stood near the middle span with both arms out.

Not dramatic.

Not frightened.

Angry in the specific way people became when infrastructure misbehaved during their shift.

I liked her immediately.

The Hanging Road crossed the south cut above a freight canal.

Not actually hanging.

Usually.

It was an old city bridge made from six broad stone spans laid over iron ribs, with pale tension plates set beneath the rail posts every twenty feet.

The plates compensated for load changes.

Broad category familiar.

Local design not.

I had crossed it plenty of times without caring.

This was what infrastructure wanted.

Nobody thinking about it.

Today it had become needy.

The stone beneath my crutch shifted sideways a finger-width.

I froze harder.

A man behind me said, "Is it breaking?"

The yellow-coat worker shouted, "No!"

Then, after a beat:

"Probably not!"

Less comforting.

Someone laughed once.

Wrong laugh.

Stress laugh.

The bridge gave another long scrape.

Below us, canal water looked much farther away than it had one minute earlier.

Because we were rising.

Not quickly.

Maybe another foot total now.

The west end remained connected to street.

The east end did too.

But the middle spans were bowing upward.

A stone bridge turning into a shallow hill while people stood on it.

That was new enough to earn my attention.

I did not analyze the plates.

This was because the woman ahead was already doing that and because dying from professional curiosity remained embarrassing.

"Stay where your feet are!" she called. "Do not run downhill. Do not bunch up. Rail only if you're already touching it."

Important.

I was not touching rail.

I stayed not touching rail.

My basket slipped slightly under arm.

Black-root rolled against wicker.

Ridiculous thing to notice.

I noticed it anyway.

There were maybe twenty people on bridge.

Not crowded.

Enough.

Two handcarts.

One small delivery mule attached to a narrow cart near west end.

The mule did not understand structural restraint.

It stepped backward.

Cart wheel rolled.

The bridge answered.

One pale tension plate under nearest rail flashed white.

The worker swore.

"Hold the mule!"

Three people near it did.

Not by grabbing animal randomly.

One took bridle.

One blocked wheel with boot.

One set cart brake.

Distributed competence.

Good.

I looked down at my own position.

Right foot flat.

Left leg absent below knee as usual.

Both crutch tips on separate stones.

My stance occupied more width than most people.

Normally useful.

Now I had three load points instead of two.

Could matter.

Maybe not.

Do not invent problem.

The yellow-coat worker moved one careful step.

Bridge twitched.

She stopped.

Looked down at tension plate beside her.

"Nobody copies me," she said.

Excellent instruction.

A second worker appeared at east end.

Orange vest.

Carrying a long forked pole with a blue ceramic jaw.

He did not step onto bridge.

He set pole against a square socket in first rail post.

Twisted.

Nothing happened.

"West lock?" he shouted.

A voice from behind me answered, "Not seated!"

"Seat it!"

"Can't under live lift!"

"Then don't!"

Again.

Competence often sounded like people refusing to do the thing they were just told to do.

The bridge rose another two inches.

Now the slope was obvious.

A woman farther ahead began sliding.

Not falling.

Her leather soles lost purchase on wet stone.

She dropped to one knee.

The person beside her caught sleeve.

The yellow-coat worker said, "Stay down. Don't stand until I tell you."

The woman stayed.

Good.

I adjusted nothing.

My right thigh had begun to burn from holding still.

Shoulders too.

Crutches were designed for movement.

Stillness on a slope used them differently.

One tip squeaked against stone.

I hated that sound.

"You with crutches," the worker called.

I looked up.

"Yes?"

"Can you hold there?"

"For now."

"If not, say before you move."

"Yes."

That was all.

No special accommodation speech.

Just useful information.

A child near east side started crying.

His father crouched but did not move feet.

"It's okay."

The child said, "It's not."

Fair.

The bridge made another scraping groan.

Then stopped rising.

Everyone waited.

Nothing.

No one cheered.

Smart crowd.

The orange-vest worker at east end shouted, "Holding!"

Yellow coat shouted back, "Temporary or caught?"

"Don't know!"

"Wonderful!"

I liked her more.

She looked at us.

"We are going to unload people in order. East side first. One person at a time from the end. Nobody passes anybody. Handcarts stay until people are off unless I say otherwise."

A man behind me said, "Why east first?"

She looked at him.

"Because that's the end I'm looking at."

He shut up.

Maybe there was a technical reason too.

Maybe not.

Sometimes a controlled plan was better than the perfect plan nobody had yet.

First person stepped off east end.

Bridge dropped half an inch.

Everyone felt it.

The crying child screamed.

Yellow coat raised hand.

"Stop."

Nobody else moved.

The bridge settled.

"Again."

Second person off.

Tiny drop.

Settle.

Third.

The process continued.

Slow.

Boring.

Wonderful.

The hill under us reduced by fractions.

Each removed body changed load enough that tension plates pulsed faintly.

Not synchronized.

Different plates responded differently.

I did not study pattern.

Mostly.

By the time east half cleared, I had become near front of remaining group without moving.

Yellow coat came closer.

Careful step.

Pause.

Careful step.

She stopped two body lengths away.

"Crutches."

"Greg."

"Greg. When I tell you, move both crutches first or foot first?"

Good question.

"Depends slope."

"This slope."

I looked.

Down toward east now.

Moderate.

Wet.

"One crutch, other crutch, right foot. Short."

"Can you stop mid-cycle?"

"Yes, but worse."

"Then complete one cycle at a time. Don't shorten after you start."

"Understood."

She pointed to stone joint three feet ahead.

"To that line. Stop."

Specific.

Good.

"Ready?"

"Yes."

"Go."

Left crutch.

Right crutch.

Right foot.

Bridge shifted slightly under third contact.

I completed cycle.

Stopped at line.

Basket pressed against ribs.

Black-root remained deeply unhelpful.

"Hold."

Held.

Plate near us dimmed.

"Again. Same length."

I moved.

Not fast.

Not elegant.

The bridge seemed more dangerous when I was allowed to move because now every sound became possibly mine.

Crutch tip.

Stone grit.

Leather.

My own breathing.

The worker did not touch me.

Did not grab crutch.

Did not try to help by changing rhythm.

Excellent.

At next line she said, "Basket?"

"Light."

"Keep it."

I appreciated not surrendering vegetables during civic crisis.

Three more cycles.

Then bridge dipped unexpectedly under right foot.

Not much.

Enough.

My balance went backward.

I drove both crutches wider.

Stopped.

Basket fell.

Black-root scattered.

Of course.

One root rolled downhill past yellow coat.

She watched it go.

Then looked at me.

"You stable?"

"Yes."

"Leave the roots."

"I was going to."

"You looked at them."

"They were expensive emotionally."

"Leave them."

"Yes."

We resumed.

At east end, stone transition between lifted bridge and street had become a four-inch step down.

Normally nothing.

With crutches on unstable span, more interesting.

Orange vest put a broad wooden wedge against edge.

Not for bridge.

For me.

"Can use this?"

I looked at wedge.

Gentler transition.

Stable on street side.

"Yes."

He planted boot behind it.

Yellow coat said, "One cycle through. Don't stop on seam."

I moved.

Crutch.

Crutch.

Foot.

Street.

The bridge behind me rose one inch.

I was off.

My body knew before mind.

Shoulders released.

Right leg shook once.

I leaned harder on crutches.

Orange vest said, "Move five steps clear."

I did.

Then turned.

People still remained.

No triumph.

No chapter-ending applause because Greg had successfully crossed a bridge he had not broken.

I stood clear and watched.

Yellow coat sent next person.

Then next.

Eventually only workers, handcarts, mule cart remained.

The handcarts came off one at a time with wheel chocks and ropes from street.

Mule cart last.

The animal had become suspicious of civilization.

Correctly.

Once people were clear, workers closed both ends with red boards.

Not yellow rope.

Red board.

Different severity.

A city clerk arrived carrying writing case.

Another worker brought two squat iron braces on a shoulder yoke.

A woman in gray inspected canal side from below.

Suddenly there were specialists everywhere.

Because this was their problem.

I sat on a low wall.

My black-root remained on bridge.

Three roots visible.

One had rolled farther.

Maybe four total.

I could wait.

The yellow-coat woman eventually came off.

She looked tired now.

Not dramatic.

Work tired.

She saw me.

"You stayed."

"My vegetables are hostages."

She looked at bridge.

"We'll get them if bridge comes down."

"If?"

"When. Probably."

"You use probably professionally."

"Frequently."

She sat two arm lengths away.

Took off one glove.

Her hand had a pale pressure mark across palm.

"What happened?" I asked.

She looked at me.

I corrected.

"What are you allowed to say happened?"

That earned half a smile.

"Center equalizer overcorrected. Or one side underreleased. Or one sensor lied. Those are guesses."

"So bridge lifted itself because it thought load changed?"

"Maybe."

"Broadly."

"Broadly, bridge is supposed to stay boring. Today it failed."

Good enough.

"Danger of falling?"

"Always."

"Helpful."

"Current danger? We don't know yet. Main ribs held. Stone joints shifted within more than normal travel but not enough that I saw a break. That's not inspection result. That's me telling you why you're alive."

"Appreciated."

A clerk approached.

"Name?"

I gave Greg.

"Injury?"

"No."

"Property lost?"

I looked at bridge.

"Black-root."

Clerk looked up.

"How much?"

"Four roots."

"Value?"

I had not counted exact bit price because civilization had progressed.

Now civilization wanted receipt.

"Ordinary market. Under a bit each."

"Claim under one copper total?"

"Much under."

"If unrecovered, household loss claim at east desk tomorrow."

"I am not filing a claim for four roots."

"Then no property claim."

He wrote.

Simple.

No encouragement either way.

"Were you instructed before moving?"

"Yes."

"By whom?"

I pointed yellow coat.

"Name?"

She said, "Sella."

There.

Name.

Not full identity.

Clerk wrote.

"Did you observe anyone running, passing, or moving a cart after stop order?"

"Mule stepped backward before cart was held."

"Anything else?"

"No."

"Thank you."

He moved on.

Sella said, "You didn't mention bridge talking."

"Bridge didn't talk."

"Good."

"It screamed structurally."

"Don't tell clerk that."

"I didn't."

We waited.

After maybe twenty minutes, workers installed both iron braces under side rail sockets.

A technician on canal bank called numbers I did not understand.

Someone at west end turned a locking wheel one notch.

The bridge lowered.

Slowly.

Stone hill relaxed toward flat.

Not all the way.

Stopped with center maybe six inches high.

Then another adjustment.

Lowered again.

This time nearly level.

No one reopened it.

Good.

A worker wearing rope harness crossed alone from west end while clipped to an overhead temporary line strung between posts.

He collected debris.

Including my roots.

One had cracked.

Three survived.

He brought basket off.

Sella handed it to me.

"One casualty."

I looked at split black-root.

"Died in service."

"Go home."

"You don't know where I live."

"Then go somewhere else."

I stood.

My right leg had stiffened while sitting.

Shoulders still tired.

Nothing injured.

I shifted basket under arm.

Three whole roots.

One cracked.

Probably edible.

Maybe more urgently edible now.

At home Cala looked at basket.

"Why is that broken?"

"Bridge tried to become a hill."

She stopped cutting cabbage.

"What?"

Tavi appeared instantly.

Of course.

"Which bridge?"

"Hanging Road."

"It hangs?"

"Apparently more than advertised."

Lyssa came in from back room.

No charcoal coat.

I did not look at that fact long enough to make it a question.

She looked at me.

"You were gone a while."

"Bridge lifted."

"Which bridge?"

"Hanging Road."

"You were on it?"

"Yes."

That changed her face.

Small.

Real.

"You okay?"

"Yes."

"Fall?"

"No."

"Anybody?"

"Not while I was there. One woman went to knee. No injury I saw."

She nodded.

Then:

"Marked door?"

I stared at her.

She smiled.

Not helpful.

"Wrong emergency."

"Still worked yesterday."

"Bridge had no door."

"Poor planning."

Tavi said, "Did it go all the way up?"

"No."

"Could boats go under it?"

"Boats could already go under it."

"Could a bigger boat go under when it goes up?"

"That is not why it goes up."

"But could it?"

I considered.

"Possibly."

"Then maybe it's for boats."

"The workers will be relieved you solved it."

"I didn't solve it. I said maybe."

Fair.

Cala took broken black-root.

Cut around crack.

"Still good."

There.

Infrastructure incident reduced to dinner.

As most things should eventually be.

Later I wrote:

HANGING ROAD CLOSED / RED BOARDS

LIFT EVENT WHILE OCCUPIED

FOLLOW WORKER MOVEMENT ORDER

DO NOT CHANGE STEP PATTERN MID-CYCLE IF AVOIDABLE

Then stopped.

That last line was too specific.

My step pattern had mattered because of my body and slope.

Not bridge doctrine.

I crossed it out.

Wrote instead:

IF INFRASTRUCTURE STARTS MOVING, LET PEOPLE WHO MAINTAIN IT OWN THE PROBLEM.

That sounded like something I should already know.

I did know it.

Knowing and doing were separate skills.

Then:

3 C CASH

26 C VALE

No artifact notes.

No magic notes.

No job lead.

No money gained.

One root cracked.

I could live with that.

Before bed, Lyssa passed my doorway.

Still no coat.

She stopped.

"You really didn't ask."

"Ask what?"

She looked at me.

I smiled.

"Growth."

"Don't ruin it."

"Stopping."

She went downstairs.

I looked at my notes.

The bridge had lifted while I was on it.

The city had gotten me off.

I had not fixed anything.

Nothing had become mine.

No one had paid me.

And somehow that still counted as a full day.

# CHAPTER 359

## THE REFUSAL

The first real offer I got from South Quay was worse than no offer.

Not morally.

That would have been easier.

It was good work.

That was the problem.

The message came after breakfast on a folded blue slip with my name written correctly.

Already suspicious.

Cala handed it to me.

"Boy left it."

"Which boy?"

"A boy."

"Useful."

"He had hair."

"Narrowing rapidly."

She went back to cutting onions.

I opened slip.

SOUTH QUAY RETURN WALL

TEMP COUNT / THREE DAYS

SECOND BELL TO LATE CART

3 C PER DAY + MEAL

REPORT BLUE SHED / MERREN

ANSWER BY MIDDAY

Nine copper.

Three days.

Meal each day.

Not guaranteed beyond that.

I read it again.

Lyssa was at table repairing a seam on one of Cala's work wraps.

She looked up.

"Work?"

"Three days."

"Good?"

"Nine copper."

Her eyebrows moved.

Not dramatically.

Enough.

"That's good."

"Yes."

I hated it immediately.

Not the work.

The timing.

There was a second note at bottom.

THIRD DAY MAY RUN PAST DARK IF WEST RETURNS ARRIVE LATE.

I had no scheduled theatre work.

No Hessa appointment written anywhere.

No immediate Vale payment.

No reason not to take it.

Which meant I needed to find one.

This was a healthy instinct.

"You're making the face," Lyssa said.

"What face?"

"The one where something is good and you start looking for why it isn't."

"I don't have that face."

Cala said, "You do."

Betrayal everywhere.

I sat.

Nine copper would take me from three to twelve.

Twelve did not buy the load lock because that was gone.

Good.

Stop thinking about load lock.

Twelve did not buy the South Quay harness either.

Also gone.

Stop shopping with hypothetical future money.

Twelve did something better.

Reserve.

It put me back above the five I had spent on Three Reeds.

It made two-copper specialist work feel different.

It made one-copper mistakes survivable.

It made Vale cash payment possible without becoming mandatory.

It made wanting another object less stupid.

Nine copper was not wealth.

It was room.

"Take it," Cala said.

I looked at her.

"You didn't even ask what it is."

"Counting boxes."

"How do you know?"

"You counted boxes last time."

"Returns."

"Boxes returned."

Close enough.

Lyssa said, "Why wouldn't you?"

"I don't know yet."

"Then that's not a reason."

Also betrayal.

I folded slip.

"I need to answer by midday."

"Then answer by midday," Cala said.

"I appreciate the procedural clarity."

"Eat first."

That was the end of family financial council.

I ate.

Then left house without answering.

Not to investigate South Quay.

Not to inspect anything.

I needed to walk.

Thinking while stationary made money feel like arithmetic.

Thinking while moving made it feel like time.

Three days.

Second bell to late cart.

Three days of leaving before midmorning and returning near dark.

Fine.

I had done worse.

First life had included months where sunrise and sunset were things other people experienced.

Second life had included theatre nights, yard work, Hessa, hauling, travel, random disasters, and one bridge trying to become a hill under me.

Three days was nothing.

So why did I dislike it?

I reached East Steps before answer arrived.

Not mystical answer.

A person.

Senna was sitting on low wall drawing a dog.

The dog was not cooperating.

It kept turning around to look at her.

"Stop being aware of art," she told it.

Dog wagged.

"Hopeless," I said.

She looked up.

"You're worse."

"I hold poses beautifully."

"You complain with your shoulders."

"That's anatomical slander."

She moved charcoal.

"Sit if you're staying."

"I'm not staying."

I stayed.

Not because Senna had plot relevance.

Because I knew her.

This was increasingly how city worked.

She drew dog.

I watched people pass.

A porter with two baskets.

A woman carrying a rolled rug.

Three children arguing whether rainwater counted as clean if it had not touched ground yet.

Reasonable question.

A man selling hot bean cakes from brass pan.

I bought nothing.

Three copper remained three copper.

Senna said, "You look employed."

"That's insulting."

"You have paper."

I had forgotten slip in hand.

"Three-day count work."

"Good?"

"Nine copper total."

She stopped drawing.

"That's good."

Everyone had same line.

"Yes."

"You don't want it?"

"I didn't say that."

"You made the face."

Unacceptable.

"What face?"

"The one where you want something and are angry at it."

Different diagnosis.

Closer.

"I want the money."

"Then take it."

"Apparently this city has one financial adviser."

She went back to dog.

"Maybe you don't want three days."

There.

Simple.

I looked at slip.

"Three days isn't much."

"Then take it."

"You are not helping."

"I am drawing a dog."

Correct.

The dog sneezed.

Senna erased its nose.

"I have other things," I said.

"Scheduled?"

"No."

"Promised?"

"No."

"Then you have imaginary things."

Rude.

"Potential things."

"Same."

I almost argued.

Then stopped.

Because she was wrong in an interesting way.

Potential things mattered.

That was reserve.

Not only money.

Time had reserve too.

If I filled three consecutive days because offer was good, I lost ability to say yes to anything else during those days.

But that alone was not reason to refuse.

Opportunity cost did not mean never commit.

It meant compare.

Compare to what?

Nothing scheduled.

Nothing promised.

Nothing urgent.

So take it.

I hated when logic ended early.

Senna said, "You can leave now."

"I was never staying."

"You have been here ten minutes."

"Temporary residence."

I stood.

Dog immediately put head in Senna's lap.

"Traitor," she told it.

I walked toward South Quay.

Not because I needed to see job.

Because answer went there.

At North Grain crossing, another person found me.

This time Saret.

He was carrying two short iron tags threaded on cord and looked annoyed enough that they were probably work.

"Greg."

"Saret."

"You get Merren's slip?"

"Yes."

"You taking it?"

"Probably."

"Good."

He started past.

Then stopped.

"Actually."

Dangerous word.

"What?"

"You free tomorrow morning?"

There it was.

Of course.

The universe waited until I had nearly made decision.

"Maybe."

"North Secondary. Not gate work."

"Then what?"

"Tag walk."

"Explain."

He shifted iron tags.

"After closure work they need independent physical read against replacement list before day route stays open. Two people. I have one. Need another who reads tags and doesn't fix numbers to be helpful."

That reputation again.

Boring correctly.

"Pay?"

"Four copper. Morning only if clean. Could run midday if not."

Four copper.

One morning.

South Quay day one was three copper plus meal.

This was more money for less time.

Obvious.

Except South Quay offered three days.

Nine total.

North Secondary offered four once.

No guarantee after.

"When do you need answer?"

"Now."

Of course.

"Why me?"

"Because I gave Merren your name and now she stole you."

"That seems like your fault."

"Yes."

He said it without shame.

"What exactly am I reading?"

"Replacement tags. Marker, location, stamped line, physical part present. You call mismatch. I call list. Neither of us signs repair."

Bounded.

Good.

"Any climbing?"

"No. Ground route. Some stairs."

"Any closed-route entry?"

"Marked work lane. Escort."

"Four copper cash?"

"Yes."

"Morning only if clean?"

"Yes."

"If not clean?"

"Same four unless supervisor expands scope. If expands, you can refuse or agree new rate."

Also good.

I looked at South Quay slip.

Three days.

Nine copper.

Reliable enough to plan.

North Secondary.

Four copper.

One morning.

Less total.

More per time.

More uncertainty after.

And it touched older thread without making me investigator.

Not that I thought in chapters.

Usually.

"Can I do North tomorrow and start South Quay day after?"

Saret shrugged.

"Ask Merren."

Right.

Adults negotiated schedules.

I had somehow forgotten this.

South Quay was not a divine command written on blue paper.

"I'm going there now."

"Tell her I stole you back."

"You are both using me incorrectly."

"Probably."

He left.

I continued to South Quay.

Now choice had shape.

Take guaranteed three days and decline North.

Take North and lose South if Merren needed exact start.

Ask.

Possibly get both.

This felt almost disappointingly functional.

Merren was at blue shed when I arrived.

She had three slips in one hand and a pencil behind ear.

"You got it."

"Yes."

"Taking it?"

"I have a conflict tomorrow morning."

She looked at me.

Not angry.

Not pleased.

Just waiting.

"Saret offered four copper for North Secondary tag walk. Morning only if clean."

"He did?"

"He says you stole me."

Merren closed eyes briefly.

"I gave him your name."

"He mentioned."

"Idiot."

"Which one?"

"Yes."

Good.

She looked at blue slips.

"I need three consecutive days because west returns are stacking. Tomorrow through third day."

There.

No both.

"Can't start day after?"

"I can put someone else on tomorrow, but then I need them all three days. Training the count rhythm twice costs me more than keeping one person."

Rational.

"So if I take North, I lose this."

"Yes."

"No offense?"

"Why would I be offended?"

"People enjoy being offended by scheduling."

"I enjoy having counters."

Fair.

"When do you need answer?"

"Now is good. Midday latest."

I looked around.

Return wall active.

Carts.

Blue shed.

Workers who already knew jobs.

Three days here would be useful.

Not exciting.

Good.

Nine copper.

Meal.

Potential future temp reliability.

North Secondary was four.

One morning.

A better rate.

More free time after.

Also older route residue I had some reason to care about.

But careful.

Interest was not compensation.

I did not owe North Secondary curiosity.

I owed myself decision.

"If I take this," I asked, "am I more likely to get future temp count work?"

Merren considered.

"If you do it well, yes."

"How much more likely?"

"I don't know."

Good answer.

"If I refuse?"

"I call next name."

"Does refusing hurt list?"

"No. Not unless you keep saying yes and then don't show."

Important.

"So declining cleanly is neutral."

"Mostly."

"Mostly?"

"If I need somebody ten times and you're never available, eventually I stop asking first. That's not punishment. That's scheduling."

Excellent.

Real consequence without morality.

"North Secondary?" she asked.

"Four copper. Morning."

"Take it."

I blinked.

"You are telling me to refuse your job?"

"I'm telling you four for a morning is better than three for a day if you don't need my three-day certainty."

"You need a counter."

"I have other names."

Again.

The world did not collapse if Greg said no.

This remained emotionally suspicious.

"Why offer me first?"

"You were good last time."

That landed harder than it should have.

Not praise.

Assessment.

Useful.

"And if I say no?"

"Then you were still good last time."

There.

I folded slip.

"I'm saying no."

"Fine."

Merren took slip back.

Crossed my name.

Wrote another.

That was it.

Nine copper disappeared without drama.

Not lost.

Declined.

Different.

My stomach still disliked distinction.

"Tomorrow," she said, "if Saret's thing falls through, don't come here expecting slot back."

"I wouldn't."

"Good."

"You think I should take North?"

"I think you already did."

She was right.

I had.

I left South Quay with same three copper I arrived with.

No job there.

No resentment.

No guarantee.

And one four-copper morning accepted verbally with Saret, assuming I could still find him.

I could.

He was where people with iron tags eventually ended up: near North Secondary office.

"I told Merren no," I said.

"Good."

"You owe me four copper tomorrow."

"Supervisor owes you."

"Less personal."

"Second bell. North office. Eat first."

"Ground route?"

"Ground route."

"Marked work lane?"

"Yes."

"If scope expands?"

"We stop and ask."

Good.

"Accepted."

He nodded.

Then handed me nothing.

No token.

No dramatic contract.

"My name is on list?"

"Now it is."

"You just wrote it?"

"Before you got here."

"What if I said no?"

"I'd cross it out."

Everyone was disturbingly comfortable with refusal today.

I went home.

Lyssa was there.

Still no charcoal coat.

I did not mention it.

She saw my face.

"You took the three days."

"No."

"Why not?"

"Got offered four copper for one morning instead."

She stopped sewing.

"You gave up nine for four?"

"Yes."

"That sounds stupid when you say it like that."

"I know."

Cala looked over from stove.

"Why?"

I explained.

Three days versus one morning.

Nine total versus four.

South Quay future probability versus North route one-off.

No punishment for clean refusal.

No guarantee after either.

Lyssa listened.

Then said, "So you bought time."

I looked at her.

"With five copper I never had."

"Basically."

That was annoyingly good.

"Maybe."

Cala said, "Four copper is still four copper."

"Tomorrow."

"If you show up."

"I intend to."

"Then eat breakfast."

Household economics remained brutally practical.

Upstairs I wrote:

SOUTH QUAY OFFER

3 DAYS / 3 C EACH + MEAL

DECLINED CLEANLY

NO LIST PENALTY STATED

Then:

NORTH SECONDARY

TAG WALK / TOMORROW / SECOND BELL

4 C / MORNING IF CLEAN

GROUND ROUTE / MARKED WORK LANE

SCOPE EXPANSION REQUIRES NEW AGREEMENT

Then my money:

3 C CASH

26 C VALE

Potential tomorrow:

+4 C

I stared at plus sign.

Crossed it out.

Not money yet.

Good.

I wrote instead:

TIME IS ALSO RESERVE.

Then immediately disliked it.

Too neat.

Too wise.

I added:

BUT RENT DOES NOT ACCEPT AFTERNOONS.

Better.

Downstairs Lyssa called, "Greg."

"What?"

"I might go back to coat shop after lunch."

I froze.

"Okay."

"That's all?"

"You told me not to say anything."

"I said in the shop."

"I'm practicing early."

Silence.

Then:

"Do you want to come?"

Danger.

I looked at notes.

Tomorrow had work.

Today did not.

Time was reserve.

Reserve could be spent.

"Yes," I said.

"Don't make faces."

"No promises."

"Then stay home."

"I can control some faces."

"Which ones?"

"None reliably."

"Greg."

"Coming."

I put paper away.

Three copper.

Four maybe tomorrow.

Nine refused.

An afternoon still mine.

Not every good offer had to become my life just because it was good.

# CHAPTER 360

## THE WRONG TAG

North Secondary looked healthier from a distance.

This was suspicious.

The last time I had been there, the route had spent most of its energy proving that roads could have administrative trauma.

Now the red closure boards were gone from the outer approach.

Yellow route markers remained.

White cord marked one narrow work lane beside the public path.

Three new iron housings sat along the left retaining wall with fresh paint around their bolts.

Nothing was smoking.

Excellent start.

I arrived before second bell.

Saret was already there.

Of course he was.

People who carried iron tags for a living developed punctuality or disappeared into paperwork.

"You ate?" he asked.

"Yes."

"Good."

"Good morning to you too."

"Morning."

That was probably his version.

He handed me a narrow slate board with six lines written in chalk.

Not numbers.

Locations.

NORTH ENTRY / WALL THREE

CUT TWO / LOW POST

OLD DRAIN TURN / INNER FACE

MARKER NINE / EAST BASE

BEND FOUR / RAIL SIDE

UPPER RETURN / GATE FOOT

"Six?"

"Six replacements."

"That's all?"

"If they match."

Dangerous sentence.

Saret had a folded paper list in one hand and three spare chalk sticks in coat pocket.

"You read physical," he said. "I read list."

"I remember."

"Say whole tag. Don't summarize."

"Marker, location, stamped line, part present."

"Yes."

"No diagnosis."

"Yes."

"No repair."

"Yes."

"No helpful corrections."

He looked at me.

"Especially that."

I liked work where competence involved restraint.

A gray-coated route supervisor met us at the entry rope.

Woman maybe fifty.

Wide face.

Braided silver hair.

Carried a long black key on cord around neck.

"Saret."

"Morning."

"Second reader?"

"Greg."

She looked at my crutches.

Then work lane.

"Ground route. Two short stair sets. Dry today. You need alternate?"

"No."

"If that changes, say it before you're tired."

Good.

"I will."

"I'm Pell. Route supervisor. You do not cross red stitch lines on stone."

She pointed.

Thin red thread had been laid into shallow grooves across several side cuts.

Not rope.

Actual thread.

Bright enough to see.

"Physical boundary?" I asked.

"Work boundary."

"Any effect?"

"No."

Excellent.

Not every red line had to be magical.

Pell unlocked side gate.

"Four copper after sign-off if completed. If one location cannot be read safely, mark unread and continue. Do not create a clean list by leaning into bad access."

I looked at Saret.

"I like her."

Pell said, "Don't."

Also excellent.

We started.

The route felt different from public side.

Not mysterious.

Maintained.

That mattered.

Public path was pale stone bordered by low walls.

Inside work lane I could see backs of route housings, cable grooves, ceramic sleeves under guard plates, little maintenance marks nobody on public side would notice.

The magic remained mostly invisible.

Infrastructure had the decency to look like infrastructure.

First location.

NORTH ENTRY / WALL THREE.

Fresh iron bracket at knee height.

Tag hung from short chain under lip.

I crouched carefully with both crutches planted wide.

Read.

"North entry. Wall three. Tag N-E-three. Replacement line A-seven. Physical housing present. Two mounting bolts visible."

Saret checked paper.

"Match."

He marked line.

That was it.

No revelation.

Second location.

CUT TWO / LOW POST.

Low post meant low.

Very low.

I sat on nearby stone edge rather than pretending nineteen-year-old pride improved geometry.

"Cut two. Low post. Tag C-two-L. Replacement line B-four. Post present. Face plate present."

"Match."

Third.

OLD DRAIN TURN / INNER FACE.

Narrower lane.

Wet wall.

One ceramic drain rib humming softly behind cover.

I did not care why.

Growth continued.

Tag was tucked under inner face near ankle height.

"Old Drain Turn. Inner face. Tag O-D-T-nine. Replacement line C-eleven. Housing present. Guard present."

Saret looked at list longer.

"Again."

I read again.

"O-D-T-nine. C-eleven."

He checked.

"Match."

"You hesitated."

"My writing is bad."

"Honest."

"Keep moving."

We did.

At first stair set, route bent upward beside retaining wall.

Seven shallow steps.

Wide.

Dry.

Saret went ahead without helping.

Good.

I took them at own pace.

Right foot.

Crutches.

Shift.

No bridge moving beneath me.

Better.

Fourth location.

MARKER NINE / EAST BASE.

This one had a new stone marker taller than my knee, iron collar at base.

Fresh pale seal compound around edge.

Tag hung from east side.

I read.

"Marker nine. East base. Tag M-nine-E. Replacement line D-six. Marker present. Collar present."

Saret stopped.

Not hesitated.

Stopped.

"Again."

I looked at him.

"M-nine-E. D-six."

He unfolded list farther.

Ran finger down column.

"List says D-eight."

There it was.

A mismatch.

Nothing exploded.

No music.

No conspiracy appeared from alley.

Just D-six on metal and D-eight on paper.

"Physical says D-six," I repeated.

"Yes."

"What now?"

"We mark mismatch."

He drew square around line.

Pell had told us to call her if mismatch affected route status.

Did this?

Not our decision.

Saret raised two fingers toward next visible worker.

Not shout.

Worker relayed signal up lane.

Pell arrived maybe three minutes later.

"Which?"

Saret showed list.

I pointed without touching.

"Marker nine east base. Physical tag M-nine-E. Replacement line D-six. List says D-eight."

Pell crouched.

Read herself.

"D-six."

"Yes."

She looked at seal compound.

Then at mounting collar.

Did not open anything.

"Was this one replaced yesterday?"

Saret said, "List says yes."

"By line D-eight."

"Yes."

Pell stood.

"Could be wrong tag. Could be wrong list. Could be wrong unit."

Three possibilities.

No conclusion.

She took black key from neck and unlocked small side box mounted on wall twenty feet away.

Inside were folded route sheets.

She pulled one.

Not maintenance record.

Installation handoff maybe.

Read.

Her mouth tightened.

"Stop read here."

Saret asked, "Whole route?"

"No. This marker only. Continue other locations. Do not count marker nine as cleared."

"Public route?"

"Public route stays current restriction until I know what was installed."

There.

Our mismatch mattered.

But not because Greg solved it.

Because accurate reading prevented a false clean sign-off.

Pell waved over two workers.

"Verify D-six versus D-eight. No opening until install sheet and physical lot agree."

One worker said, "Could just be tag left from old."

Pell said, "Could. Verify."

No one rewarded speculation.

Civilization survived.

Saret marked:

MISMATCH / HOLD CLEARANCE

Then looked at me.

"Next."

I liked him too.

Procedurally.

Fifth location.

BEND FOUR / RAIL SIDE.

We walked farther uphill.

The route here opened over shallow drainage channel.

Not canal.

Stone cut with pale water moving beneath slotted covers.

Rail-side housing was new brass-faced rectangle with three narrow blue windows.

One window flickered.

I did not ask.

Actually I did.

"Is flicker normal?"

Saret said, "I don't know."

Good.

"Does it affect tag read?"

"No."

Better.

I read tag.

"Bend four. Rail side. Tag B-four-R. Replacement line F-two. Housing present. Guard rail clear."

"Match."

Sixth location.

UPPER RETURN / GATE FOOT.

Second stair set.

Only four steps but steeper.

Pell's warning returned.

Say something before tired.

I wasn't tired enough to need alternate.

But shoulders had started noticing morning.

I told Saret.

"Slower on stairs. Fine otherwise."

"Fine."

No drama.

He simply slowed.

Upper return had old gate frame with one new dark-gray foot plate.

Tag was clean.

"Upper return. Gate foot. Tag U-R-G. Replacement line G-five. Foot plate present. Three anchor points visible."

Saret checked.

"Match."

He folded paper.

"Done."

It was not even midday.

There was something deeply satisfying about a job ending when advertised.

We returned to marker nine.

Pell and two workers were still there.

One held small tray containing two iron tags.

Old and new maybe.

I did not approach until Pell waved.

"Your read stands," she said.

"Good."

"Install sheet says D-eight. Physical lot stamp on collar says D-eight. Tag says D-six."

Saret asked, "Wrong tag?"

"Looks like."

Looks like.

Still bounded.

Pell continued, "We're replacing tag and re-running independent read after. You two are done."

I looked at collar.

"So unit is probably correct?"

"Probably. Not your sign-off."

"Right."

Good correction.

Pell looked at me.

"You were hired to read. You read."

This city kept finding ways to praise me that sounded like instructions.

I liked those best.

At office, clerk had two columns.

Saret signed one.

I signed one.

Not repair certification.

Work completion only.

Pell signed supervisor line with note:

6 LOCATIONS VISITED

5 MATCH

1 TAG/LIST MISMATCH HELD FROM CLEARANCE

FOLLOW-UP REQUIRED

Then clerk counted four copper into my hand.

Actual money.

Heavy enough to exist.

I counted once.

Four.

Three in purse already.

Seven total.

No bits.

There.

Yesterday's crossed-out plus sign became real only now.

I liked that too.

Saret got paid separately.

I did not ask how much.

Another growth miracle.

Outside office, he said, "You free next week?"

I looked at him.

"For what?"

"Nothing yet."

"Then no."

He frowned.

"You don't know."

"Exactly."

He almost smiled.

Almost.

"Fine. If something exists, I'll ask."

"Good system."

We separated.

The rest of morning belonged to me.

This was the part I had purchased with five copper I never earned.

No.

Bad sentence.

I had accepted less total guaranteed money for this.

Different.

Still real.

I could go home.

Could find Senna.

Could check whether Hanging Road reopened.

Did not.

Could go near market.

Dangerous.

Did not.

Could retrieve Three Reeds and invent a reason to use it.

Absolutely not.

I bought lunch instead.

One bit.

A bowl of lentils with smoked onion and flatbread.

Ordinary spending.

No crisis.

No calculation beyond wanting lunch and having money.

This was maybe economic progression too.

I sat outside under awning and ate while workers passed.

A pair of route hands carried a replacement marker in sling.

Not ours.

Probably.

I did not follow them.

After lunch I walked home slowly.

Lyssa was not there.

Charcoal coat also not there.

Cala said, "She went out."

I looked at her.

"She'll tell you herself."

"I didn't ask."

"Then we're done."

Tavi was on floor building spoon carrier again.

It had acquired third wheel.

"Where's Lyssa?" he asked.

I looked at Cala.

"Out."

Tavi nodded.

Reasonable child.

I went upstairs.

Opened notes.

Wrote:

NORTH SECONDARY / TAG WALK COMPLETE

6 LOCATIONS

5 MATCH

1 MISMATCH

MARKER 9 EAST BASE

PHYSICAL TAG D-6 / LIST D-8

HELD FROM CLEARANCE

SUPERVISOR FOLLOW-UP

Then:

PAID 4 C

CASH NOW 7 C

26 C VALE

I paused.

Seven.

Not twelve.

Not the nine-plus-three I would have after South Quay if three days already passed.

Those days had not passed.

Comparison was fake.

Today I had seven and an afternoon.

That was current reality.

I added:

READING CORRECTLY CAN BE ENOUGH.

Then stared.

Too neat again.

I crossed it out.

Wrote instead:

DON'T FIX A MISMATCH BY MAKING THE PAPER FEEL BETTER.

Better.

Downstairs door opened.

Lyssa came in.

I heard Cala say nothing.

Then Tavi shouted, "YOU BOUGHT IT."

I closed eyes.

There it was.

Not my decision.

Not my ledger.

Still interesting.

I went downstairs.

Lyssa stood by door wearing charcoal coat.

High collar folded down.

Dark horn buttons.

Deep pockets.

It fit even better after alteration.

She looked at me.

I remembered instruction.

No talking.

No faces.

We were not in shop anymore.

Still.

I said nothing.

She waited.

I kept face neutral.

This became painful.

"Well?" she said.

"You told me not to make faces."

"In the shop."

"I'm being respectful beyond contract."

"Greg."

"It looks good."

She tried not to smile.

Failed slightly.

"Nine?" I asked.

Then immediately regretted it.

Her eyebrow rose.

"You said my money isn't your ledger."

"Correct. Withdrawn."

"Eight and six bits."

I blinked.

"You negotiated."

"I waited."

"Different weapon."

"He had someone else try it. Sleeves were wrong. I came back. He offered eight and six bits if I took it with sleeve adjustment only."

"And you did."

"Yes."

"Happy?"

She thought.

Actually thought.

"Yes."

Good.

That was enough.

Cala touched sleeve.

"Good wool."

Lyssa said, "I know."

Tavi put both hands into one pocket.

"This pocket is huge."

"Get out of my coat."

"I'm checking it."

"You don't live in there."

"I could put spoon carrier in it."

"No."

"Only little one."

"No."

His speech continued while she removed him from pocket.

I watched.

Seven copper in my purse.

Lyssa had bought something she wanted without asking permission from Greg's invisible economic council.

North Secondary still had one held tag mismatch and one route supervisor who owned problem.

South Quay had another counter.

Carrow continued.

Nothing required me at center.

This was becoming suspiciously comfortable.

Later, before dinner, I added one more line to notes:

AFTERNOON USED WELL.

Then crossed it out too.

I had not used afternoon.

It had simply happened.

That was allowed.

# CHAPTER 361

## THE MODEL

Senna found me because apparently sitting still had become a marketable skill.

This was insulting for several reasons.

I was halfway through breakfast when somebody knocked three times, waited, then knocked twice more like the door had failed an examination.

Cala looked at me.

"Your person."

"How do you know?"

"Mine knock once."

Reasonable system.

I opened door.

Senna stood outside with charcoal on one cheek, a rolled cloth under one arm, and the expression of somebody whose morning had already become other people's fault.

"Good," she said.

"Hello."

"You're here."

"Disturbingly."

"I need your leg."

I looked down.

"This one?"

"The missing one."

"Harder to lend."

"Not like that."

"You opened poorly."

She pushed past me.

Cala said, "Tea?"

"Please."

Traitor.

Lyssa was at work table wearing the charcoal coat despite being indoors.

Not fully.

Open front, sleeves still settling into new shape.

She had worn it twice since buying it and each time behaved as if nobody should notice.

Everyone noticed.

Tavi noticed most.

He had already asked whether the pockets could carry bread, a hammer, a small bird, and him.

The answer had remained no in increasingly specific ways.

Senna looked at coat.

"That's good."

Lyssa said, "I know."

No shame now.

Good.

Senna dropped rolled cloth onto chair.

"I need Greg for two hours."

I said, "You do not own two hours."

"I can pay."

That changed sentence structure.

"Continue."

"One copper."

"For what?"

"Sitting."

"I am excellent at that."

Lyssa said, "You complain while sitting."

"Premium feature."

Senna unrolled cloth.

Inside were six large sheets of heavy gray drawing paper and three thinner sheets covered in quick body studies.

Not portraits.

Positions.

A shoulder carrying a basket.

A man crouched with one heel lifted.

An old woman leaning into a cane while turning.

A child asleep against somebody's chest.

Then one unfinished figure with two crutches.

No face.

No clothes beyond rough lines.

Left leg ended below knee.

Not me exactly.

Close enough to become suspicious.

"You drew me from memory."

"Badly."

"Comforting."

She tapped unfinished figure.

"I need weight transfer."

I looked again.

The drawing had both crutches planted wide and right foot too centered.

The body floated between them.

No load.

No reason.

"That's wrong."

"I know."

"Then why show me?"

"Because I know it's wrong and don't know which wrong."

Better.

I sat at table.

Senna drank Cala's tea in three quick swallows that suggested temperature was somebody else's problem.

"What is this for?" I asked.

"Guild wall."

"Which guild?"

"South Cloth Mutual."

Lyssa looked up.

"Why?"

Senna pointed at studies.

"They want working bodies. Not trade portraits. Carrying, standing, reaching, resting. Twelve panels around new stair hall."

"Why?" I asked again.

"Because somebody with money decided workers like pictures of workers."

Lyssa said, "Do they?"

"They like walls less when empty, apparently."

Good artist.

Senna continued, "They asked for age range, body range, different work. One panel has crutches."

I looked at her.

"Because diversity?"

She made a face.

"Because the stair hall also has ramp entrance and somebody on committee noticed their first sketch had twelve people carrying things up stairs."

I laughed.

Could not help it.

"Excellent."

"I said if they want a crutch user, I need one who actually stands on crutches."

"There are others."

"Yes."

"Why me?"

"I know you."

There it was again.

Not destiny.

Proximity.

"And you sit long enough."

"Less flattering."

"One copper. Two hours. Breaks when you need. No face if you don't want. Clothes stay on."

"Generous."

"I learned to specify."

I did not ask from what.

Cala did.

"From what?"

Senna stared into tea.

"Artists."

Enough.

I considered money.

Seven copper.

One more would be eight.

No bits involved.

Good.

I considered time.

Two hours.

No scheduled work.

No Hessa note.

No reason to protect entire day simply because I had recently discovered optionality.

That would become its own stupidity.

"Where?"

"Old dye court. South Cloth has room with north windows."

Lyssa said, "Flat floor?"

Senna nodded.

"Wide door. Bench."

She looked at me.

"I checked."

That landed quietly.

Not heroic.

Not praise.

She had checked whether place worked before asking me to work there.

Good.

"One copper," I said. "Two hours."

"Yes."

"If you need longer, ask before longer."

"Yes."

"Breaks don't extend clock unless I agree."

Senna frowned.

"I was going to say breaks don't count."

"Then one copper for two working hours?"

"Yes."

"Different offer."

She thought.

"Two working hours. Breaks outside."

"Fine."

Lyssa said, "Look at him negotiate sitting."

"Every profession matures."

Tavi asked, "Can I get paid to sit?"

Cala said, "You can start free."

He sat immediately.

"Now what?"

"Stay."

He lasted perhaps four seconds.

Competitive field.

I left with Senna after breakfast.

I did not bring Three Reeds.

Did not bring holdfast.

Did not bring rest stone.

Oval stayed in shoulder bag because that was where oval lived now, which was not same as bringing it for work.

Seven copper stayed in purse.

The city was bright after two days of thin cloud.

Hanging Road remained closed at west approach.

I could see closure board from two streets away.

Did not detour.

North Secondary was not on route.

Good.

Old dye court sat behind South Cloth hall in district I knew mostly by smell.

Soap.

Wet wool.

Dye bark.

Hot water.

Metallic bite from mordant vats somewhere behind walls.

The guild building was not impressive.

Three stories of pale brick with dark timber braces and wide loading doors.

The new stair hall attached to east side in cleaner stone.

Ramp curved around outer wall instead of cutting straight up.

Not elegant.

Usable.

I appreciated buildings that admitted bodies had geometry.

Senna led me through side door.

A clerk at desk looked at my crutches, then at Senna.

Not suspicious.

Just checking.

"Model?"

Senna said, "Yes."

There.

Role acquired.

The clerk wrote something.

"Second room. North windows. Keep charcoal off new floor."

Senna looked offended.

"I know."

Clerk looked at her cheek.

Senna wiped wrong side.

Charcoal remained.

I said nothing.

Professional solidarity.

Second room was better than promised.

Wide.

High windows.

Pale daylight.

A long bench against wall.

Two straight chairs.

One low platform maybe six inches high.

I looked at platform.

Senna said, "You don't need to use it."

"Good."

"I thought maybe seated pose later."

"Fine."

"First standing."

Less fine.

She set paper on tall board.

Charcoal sticks in shallow tray.

No magical art tool.

No hidden artifact.

Just work.

"What do you need?" I asked.

"Natural standing rest first."

"With both crutches?"

"Whatever natural means."

Dangerous instruction.

I stood how I stood when waiting.

Right foot under me.

Left knee slightly forward.

Both crutches not symmetrical.

Right crutch farther out.

Left closer.

Shoulders not level because bodies were rude.

Senna stared.

Then moved my unfinished sheet aside and started new one.

"Don't pose."

"I'm not."

"You are."

"I became aware of being observed."

"Stop."

"Excellent advice."

She drew.

Silence lasted almost a minute.

Then:

"You lean more than I remembered."

"Which way?"

"Right."

"Usually."

"Why?"

"Because that's where foot is."

She looked annoyed.

"More than that."

I thought.

"Depends on rest. If I'm actually waiting, I let right leg take more and use crutches to widen base. If I'm about to move, weight changes before first crutch does."

"Show me."

I did.

Not step.

Preparation.

Small forward shift.

Shoulders changed.

Hands loaded differently.

Right knee unlocked.

Senna's charcoal moved fast.

"Again."

I reset.

Shifted.

"Again."

Reset.

Shifted.

By fourth, it stopped being natural because repetition makes every movement stupid.

"Enough," she said.

Good.

She turned paper.

"Now start a step and stop before foot moves."

"Crutch moves first sometimes."

"Then that."

I set left crutch slightly forward.

Right followed enough to open path.

Body leaned.

"Hold."

I held.

This was not a comfortable static position.

"How long?"

"Thirty breaths."

"Whose?"

"Mine."

"You breathe badly."

"Twenty."

Negotiation successful.

She drew.

At breath twelve my right thigh started complaining.

At fifteen hand pressure increased.

At eighteen I said, "Done."

She said, "Two more."

"No."

I reset.

Senna blinked.

Then nodded.

Good.

No artistic suffering doctrine.

She looked at sketch.

"Your hand changed."

"Yes."

"Before leg?"

"Probably."

"Can we do shorter holds and I watch hand?"

"Yes."

There.

Better question.

We did six short starts.

No counts in manuscript state because this was not Hessa and civilization had limits.

Senna watched fingers.

Crutch grip.

Shoulder.

Right foot.

The strange empty line below left knee where other bodies would have another contact point.

She stopped trying to make me symmetrical.

Drawing improved immediately.

I could see it even unfinished.

Not because I was art expert.

Because figure finally looked like it would fall if you removed crutches.

Which meant crutches mattered.

"There," I said.

"What?"

"Now they're doing something."

She looked at page.

"Yes."

"Before they were accessories."

Her mouth tightened.

Not defensive.

Thinking.

"That's what was wrong."

"Part of it."

"What else?"

I pointed at torso without touching paper.

"You drew missing foot, not missing load path."

She stared at me.

I regretted sentence because it sounded intelligent.

"Say that again."

"No."

"Greg."

"You removed lower leg from ordinary standing person. Body doesn't work like ordinary standing person minus one piece. Whole balance changed."

"That's better."

"Less quotable."

"More useful."

She wrote something tiny at page edge.

I did not ask.

Maybe insult.

Then door opened.

A woman in blue work apron entered carrying three folded cloth samples.

She stopped.

"Oh. Sorry."

Senna said, "We're working."

"I can see."

Woman looked at me.

Not long.

Enough to register crutches.

Then at drawing.

"That's the stair panel?"

Senna said, "Maybe."

"Committee wanted carrying."

I waited.

Senna's face changed.

Not Greg dry.

Hers got longer when irritated.

"Committee wanted working bodies," she said. "He can carry if you want to hire him carrying. Today I hired him to stand and move because I need the mechanics right before I invent a task around them."

Woman lifted hands.

"I wasn't criticizing."

"You were about to ask why he isn't holding cloth."

"I was going to ask whether he could."

Senna looked at me.

Good.

She did not answer for me.

I said, "Yes. Depending what and how much."

Woman nodded.

"Could you carry folded length under one arm?"

"With crutches?"

She looked at my hands.

Realized problem.

"Right."

Not mockery.

Actual correction.

"Shoulder bag," I said. "Back load. Sling. Something attached. Not under arm while using two crutches unless I'm stationary."

"What about over shoulder?"

"Maybe if balanced and not interfering with crutch swing. Depends weight."

Senna said, "That is why I'm not inventing carrying yet."

Woman sighed.

"Fine. You're right."

Senna blinked.

Apparently this was unexpected.

Woman put cloth samples on far table.

"Committee meets in six days. They want first three panels."

"They'll have them."

"Including this one?"

Senna looked at sketch.

Then me.

"Maybe."

Woman left.

Door closed.

I said, "You enjoyed that."

"No."

"You did."

"A little."

There.

Human.

We took break.

I sat on bench.

Senna ate half an apple from bag and spoke around it.

Messily.

"They keep wanting symbols before bodies. Potter holding pot. Weaver holding cloth. Carrier holding box. Old person holding cane. Then they complain everybody looks like signs over shop doors."

"Because they are."

"Yes."

"You could draw Greg holding Greg."

She stared.

"What does that mean?"

"No idea."

"I hate you."

"One copper."

"I hate you professionally."

Better.

Break lasted until my hands felt ordinary again.

Second working hour was seated and transitional.

Senna had me sit on straight chair, crutches within reach, then stand naturally.

First attempt she stopped me halfway.

"That."

"That is worst place."

"I know. Five breaths."

I held four.

She accepted four.

Again.

This time I showed how right foot moved back before push.

How one crutch could brace while other hand used chair if chair stable.

How I hated chairs that slid.

She tested chair with foot.

"This one slides."

"Yes."

"Then don't use arm?"

"Correct."

"Both crutches?"

"Usually."

She drew.

Then asked me to rest seated without arranging myself.

I did.

She frowned.

"You put crutches where you can reach without looking."

"Yes."

"Always?"

"Usually."

"Why?"

I looked at her.

"Because otherwise I can't reach them without looking."

She threw charcoal nub at me.

Missed.

Art remained dangerous.

By end of two working hours, floor around Senna looked like charcoal had attempted escape.

New floor survived mostly.

She had nine sheets.

Three I thought good.

Four I could not judge.

Two made me look like a wounded crane.

I told her.

"Those are movement notes."

"Burn them."

"No."

"My reputation."

"You have one?"

Hostile workplace.

She counted one copper into my hand.

One.

No bits.

No ambiguity.

"Eight," I said.

"What?"

"Nothing."

I put it away.

Current cash eight copper.

Vale twenty-six.

No reason to say either aloud.

Senna rolled finished studies separately from rough ones.

"If committee uses your panel, they may want final pose again."

"Paid?"

"Yes."

"Then ask when it exists."

She looked at me.

"Saret?"

"How do you know Saret?"

"I don't. You said that like somebody recently trained you."

I hated reputation again.

"Ask when it exists," I repeated.

"Fine."

Outside South Cloth hall, afternoon had gone warm.

Not hot.

Enough that stone smelled dry.

Senna headed east with drawings.

I headed nowhere for a while.

Eight copper.

Not huge.

More than seven.

More interesting was why I had it.

Not hauling.

Not counting.

Not fighting.

Not magic.

Not artifact expertise.

My body itself had been useful information.

That thought could become sentimental if mishandled.

I handled it badly on purpose.

Apparently being shaped inconveniently had a consulting rate.

One copper per two hours.

There.

Much safer.

At home, Lyssa had finally taken coat off.

It hung from proper peg instead of chair because Cala had apparently declared civilization.

Tavi's spoon carrier sat beneath it.

Not inside pocket.

Victory.

Lyssa looked at my hands.

"Charcoal."

I looked.

Black dust on right palm.

"Senna threw art at me."

"Why?"

"I was helping."

"That explains it."

I washed.

Then told them what job had been.

Cala understood immediately.

"She paid you to stand."

"And sit."

"Good work."

"I have found my calling."

Lyssa asked, "What is she drawing?"

"Guild wall. Workers. Apparently committees discover bodies in stages."

I explained crutches as accessories problem.

Lyssa's expression changed.

"People do that."

"Do what?"

"Put something on a person so you know what kind of person they are."

"Coat?"

She looked at me.

"Don't ruin it."

"Stopping."

She continued after a moment.

"They draw a seamstress with needle. A soldier with sword. A mother with baby. Then that's all they are in picture."

"Efficient signage."

"Bad people."

"Bad pictures."

"Same thing sometimes."

Not sure I agreed.

Did not need to.

Tavi asked, "What would mine be?"

I said, "Spoon carrier."

He looked offended.

"I do other things."

Lyssa and I looked at each other.

There it was.

Tavi, accidental philosopher.

"Exactly," Lyssa said.

He nodded as if he had won argument he did not know existed.

Then picked up spoon carrier and crashed it into wall.

Philosophy ended.

Upstairs I opened notes.

Wrote:

SENNA / SOUTH CLOTH STUDIES

2 WORKING HOURS

PAID 1 C

CASH 8 C

26 C VALE

Then stopped.

I almost wrote something about load paths.

Didn't.

That belonged to Senna's work now.

Almost wrote something about bodies being information.

Too grand.

Almost wrote:

MODEL.

I did write that.

Then underneath:

TEMPORARILY.

Better.

Roles were dangerous when you forgot they ended.

# CHAPTER 362

## THE LOOKOUT

I became a lookout because Tavi wanted to see a fish fly.

This was not a metaphor.

Carrow had actual flying fish.

Apparently nobody had considered telling me.

"They're not fish," Lyssa said.

Tavi was standing on a chair at breakfast, which meant Cala had already told him to sit down at least once.

"They are," he said.

"They're river kites."

"Kites aren't alive."

"Those are."

"Then fish."

"That is not how categories work."

I looked at Cala.

"What are river kites?"

She looked surprised.

"You don't know?"

"I have successfully not known for nineteen years."

"They come through lower spill after first warm rain."

"Doing what?"

Tavi answered before she could.

"Flying."

Useful.

Cala said, "Gliding. Mostly."

Lyssa tore bread.

"Jump from water, catch the lift off spill channels, land upriver."

I stopped eating.

"Why?"

Three people looked at me.

Not because question was stupid.

Because apparently this was childhood knowledge.

Tavi said, "Because they're fish."

That explained nothing and satisfied him completely.

"Breeding run," Cala said. "Or feeding. Depends who you ask."

Better.

"Magical?"

Lyssa shrugged.

"A little."

Carrow's relationship with magic remained offensive.

A little.

Like seasoning.

"How little?"

Cala pointed spoon at me.

"You can go look without making it work."

I had been known too long.

Tavi bounced on chair.

"We have to go before second bell because Naro said they were already at lower gates and if we miss them then they go past mill turn and you can't see them good because roofs and also his brother said one hit a woman in face last year but he lies sometimes, except maybe not about fish because why would you lie about fish when real fish already fly?"

There.

Tavi speech had become a cart rolling downhill.

I liked it.

"Who is Naro?" I asked.

"Naro."

"Excellent."

Cala said, "Boy two doors east."

"I know his mother," Lyssa said.

"Do I?"

"No."

Good.

I finished breakfast.

Eight copper stayed in purse.

No work scheduled.

No reason not to go watch magical fish make poor decisions.

Tavi was already at door.

"Shoes," Cala said.

He looked down.

Bare feet.

"I knew."

"Then do it."

He ran upstairs.

Lyssa said, "You taking him?"

"Apparently."

"I was asking Cala."

Cala said, "Greg can."

I looked between them.

"Interesting transfer of authority."

"Bring him back," Cala said.

"Alive?"

She looked at me.

"Greg."

"Alive."

Tavi returned with shoes on wrong feet.

I pointed.

He looked.

"They still fit."

"That is not the standard."

He changed them while standing.

Nearly fell.

Caught wall.

I waited.

"Sit," I said.

"I can do it."

"You demonstrated. Sit."

He sat.

Shoes corrected.

We left.

I had taken Tavi places before.

Not often enough to have system.

Enough to know children did not move through city like adults.

Adults selected destination and route.

Children selected every object between destination and route.

A blue bottle cap.

A dog with one white ear.

A chalk circle somebody had drawn around a drain.

A man carrying six chairs.

"Why six?"

"Maybe he needs six chairs."

"But why all now?"

"Because carrying one six times is worse."

"What if he drops them?"

"Then carrying one six times becomes competitive."

Tavi watched man until corner hid him.

Then ran three steps to catch up.

"Walking," I said.

"I am."

"That was running."

"Only little."

"Still running."

"You can't run."

I looked at him.

He looked at my crutches.

Not insult.

Observation.

"Correct."

"Can you run with those?"

"Badly."

"Show me."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I enjoy having teeth."

He considered.

"You could run without falling."

"Probably."

"Then show me."

"No."

"Why?"

This was going well.

Lower spill sat beyond dye lanes where two old water channels came down from east cut and split around a wedge of warehouses.

I had crossed upper bridges.

Never stood at lower gates during fish season.

Apparently season was three mornings long some years and ten days others.

Nobody agreed.

Crowd told me we had found it before water did.

Families lined stone rail.

Children on shoulders.

Vendors with hot buns and paper cones of salted seeds.

Two city workers in green vests had roped off the lowest ledge.

Not because fish were dangerous.

Because people were idiots near moving water.

Comforting.

Tavi stopped.

"There."

I saw nothing.

Then water exploded.

A silver shape came out of spill channel at an angle no fish had business attempting.

Longer than my hand.

Flat fins snapped wide.

Not wings.

Close enough to offend taxonomy.

Blue-white lines lit along its sides.

It climbed.

Actually climbed.

Not much.

Maybe ten feet above water.

Then glided across lower basin, curved in air, and hit upper channel with a splash.

Crowd cheered.

I stared.

Tavi looked at me with enormous satisfaction.

"Fish."

"Fine."

"Say it."

"Fish."

"Flying fish."

"River kite."

"Greg."

"You sound like Lyssa."

"Flying fish."

Another launched.

Then three.

One failed badly and slapped sideways into stone lip before flopping back into current.

Nobody gasped.

It recovered.

Tried again twenty seconds later.

Successful.

I loved it immediately.

Not because artifact.

Not because useful.

Because world had apparently hidden ridiculous fish from me and expected me to accept this.

Blue-white side lines brightened only during air phase.

Maybe field interaction.

Maybe body-generated lift.

Maybe something in water.

I stopped.

Cala's spoon pointed at me in memory.

You can go look without making it work.

Fine.

I looked.

Tavi squeezed between two adults toward rail.

I caught back of his shirt.

"No."

"I can see."

"From here."

"People are tall."

"Cruel world."

"I can't see."

That was true.

Crowd had thickened.

I looked around.

There was a low freight step beside warehouse wall, maybe a foot high.

Bad for me.

Good for him.
