Also crowded.

A stack of empty produce crates sat farther back.

Absolutely not.

Tavi saw them same moment.

"I can stand on those."

"No."

"They're strong."

"No."

"You didn't check."

"I checked spiritually."

"That's not checking."

Damn child.

A woman beside us laughed.

She had a baby tied against chest and a girl maybe eight holding sleeve.

"He's right," she said.

"Please don't strengthen him."

"Sorry."

She was not sorry.

Tavi pointed at her.

"She says."

"She is not in charge of you."

Woman said, "Definitely not."

Good.

A city worker moved along rope.

"Keep children behind yellow stones. No crates, no rail sitting. If you need a viewing step, west wall has public blocks."

There.

Infrastructure defeated improvisation.

"Public blocks," I said.

Tavi groaned like government had ruined childhood.

We moved west.

Public blocks were three broad stone risers built into wall.

Not seating exactly.

Viewing steps.

Children occupied most.

Parents behind.

I found Tavi space on lowest block.

"Stay on this one."

"Second is better."

"Lowest."

"Why?"

"Because I can reach you without climbing."

He looked at my leg.

Then at step.

"Okay."

No argument.

That landed strangely.

Not pity.

Geometry.

He climbed lowest block and immediately forgot me because six fish launched together.

For ten minutes I was not Greg the collector or model or reader or whatever else city had temporarily named me.

I was an adult standing behind a child at water.

This turned out to involve more work than expected.

"Don't lean."

"I'm not."

"Your entire body is past your feet."

"But I'm not falling."

"That is a temporary success condition."

"Look!"

I looked.

A fish came high.

Higher than others.

Blue-white side light bright enough to see in daylight.

It crossed over rope line.

Over crowd.

People ducked.

The fish did not.

It hit canvas awning over seed vendor.

THUMP.

Canvas sagged.

Fish flopped once.

Vendor shouted something impolite.

Then fish slid down canvas, launched off edge somehow, and smacked a man in shoulder.

Crowd erupted.

Man caught fish reflexively.

This was wrong move.

The side lines flashed.

His hair lifted straight up.

He yelled and dropped it.

Fish hit street, flopped twice, then skitter-glided under rope and back into water.

Tavi was vibrating.

"Naro didn't lie!"

"About woman in face?"

"Fish hit man. Same."

"Those are different people and body parts."

"But fish hit."

Fair.

Green-vest worker shouted, "Do not catch kites! Let them fall!"

The vendor was inspecting awning.

Man's hair slowly lowered.

No injury beyond dignity.

Good.

Tavi turned to me.

"If one hits us, can I touch it?"

"No."

"What if it can't get back?"

"Worker."

"What if worker isn't there?"

"We find one."

"What if all workers leave?"

"Why would all workers leave during fish?"

"Maybe lunch."

"Then fish have won."

He accepted this.

Crowd shifted.

Somebody behind us said, "Big run coming."

I looked toward lower channel.

Water had changed.

Not level.

Texture.

Silver flashes beneath surface.

Dozens.

Maybe more.

Blue-white light flickered under water like broken reflection.

Workers started moving.

Not panic.

Procedure.

"Children down one step," one called. "Keep west lane open. Big run."

Parents repeated it.

Children complained.

Tavi stayed lowest already.

Good decision paid dividends.

I moved closer behind him.

Not touching.

Ready.

First wave launched.

Eight.

Ten.

Hard to count.

Then second.

The air over basin filled with silver bodies.

Some low.

Some high.

Blue-white lines made arcs.

Not organized.

Not school formation.

Chaos with direction.

Every fish wanted upstream and possessed a different opinion about route.

People shouted.

Laughed.

Duck calls moved through crowd.

One fish clipped rope post.

Another landed in empty bun basket and immediately left.

Tavi screamed with joy.

Then he stepped down.

Not toward water.

Sideways.

Following one fish that had landed behind viewing blocks.

I caught shoulder.

"Stay."

"It can't get up."

Fish flopped against wall.

Blue lines flashed.

No clear path to water because crowd feet.

Tavi pulled against my hand.

"Greg."

"Stay."

"It'll die."

Maybe.

I looked for worker.

Nearest green vest was twenty feet away managing rope.

I raised voice.

"Kite behind west blocks!"

Worker heard.

Pointed at another worker farther back.

That one came carrying long shallow net with wooden handle.

Good.

Tavi watched.

Worker did not touch fish.

Slid net under it.

Fish flashed hard enough that net rim glowed pale for a second.

Worker angled it over wall gap.

Fish launched before reaching water and vanished into basin.

Tavi exhaled.

"See?"

I said.

"We helped."

"You stayed. I yelled. Worker helped."

"That's helping."

I considered.

"Yes."

He looked pleased.

Not because he rescued fish.

Because he had been part of correct chain.

Good.

Big run lasted maybe five minutes.

Felt longer.

Then water settled.

Crowd loosened.

People began leaving.

Tavi did not.

"More."

"Probably."

"We wait."

"How long?"

"Until more."

Children understood scheduling differently.

We waited.

I bought nothing because I had eaten breakfast and did not need bun merely because bun existed.

Economic heroism.

Tavi stared at water.

Then asked, "Why do they fly?"

There it was.

I could give broad first-life category.

Mana-assisted migration behavior existed.

Aquatic creatures using field pressure, thermal gradients, air currents, buoyancy changes, temporary fin stiffening, ridiculous evolutionary solutions to geography.

I had seen worse.

I had eaten worse.

Probably.

But I did not know these.

"I don't know," I said.

Then corrected because old habit of sounding ignorant when I knew broad family was also stupid.

"I know ways animals can use mana to move. I don't know which way these do it."

Tavi nodded.

"They jump."

"Yes."

"Then fly."

"Glide."

"Fly."

"Fine."

"So that's how."

He had solved ecology.

I envied him.

A man on upper viewing step leaned down.

Older, white beard, river boots.

"They ride spill lift."

I looked at him.

"Air?"

"Partly. Water throws cold field off lower gate. Warm air comes over stone. Kites catch both."

Maybe.

"You work river?"

"Thirty-two years."

Better source.

"Do side lines create lift?"

He shrugged.

"Lines stiffen fins. Maybe more. Cut one open, lines go dark."

"People cut them open?"

"People eat them."

Tavi looked horrified.

Old man looked at him.

"Not during run."

Tavi remained horrified.

"Ever see them without warm rain?" I asked.

"Small jumps. Not this."

"Why upstream?"

"Egg beds above mill turn."

There.

Local implementation.

Enough.

Not full mechanism.

I stopped asking before observation became interview.

Old man returned to water.

Tavi whispered, "He eats them."

"Probably not these exact ones."

"Fish family."

"You eat chickens."

"Not chickens I know."

Reasonable ethical framework.

Another run came smaller.

Tavi watched every fish.

I watched Tavi half the time.

That was role.

Not expert.

Not investigator.

Lookout.

I was there to notice when his feet moved where they should not.

This became relevant again when Naro appeared.

I identified him by process of elimination.

A boy about Tavi's age came sprinting through west lane yelling, "TAVI!"

Tavi yelled back.

"NARO!"

Science.

Naro had older sister behind him saying, "Stop running," with exhausted precision of someone who had said it since birth.

He stopped only because Tavi existed.

"You saw face fish?"

Tavi said, "Man shoulder."

"I told you."

"You said woman face."

"Last year."

"Greg says different."

Naro looked at me.

"Are you Greg?"

"Unfortunately."

"Tavi says you have magic things."

Tavi froze.

I looked at him.

He looked at water.

Interesting.

"Does he?"

Naro nodded.

"A stone that knows doors."

That was not ideal.

Tavi said quickly, "It doesn't know doors."

"You said."

"I said it clicks."

"At doors."

"Sometimes not doors."

This was becoming peer-reviewed badly.

I crouched as much as crutches allowed, which was not much.

"Tavi."

He looked at me.

"House rule isn't secret magic treasure. But we also don't tell people what is in locked chest."

His face changed.

Worry.

"I didn't tell chest."

"I know."

"Only oval."

"Oval travels with me, so people can see it. Still better not to explain our stuff to everybody."

"Why?"

Naro listened with professional interest.

"Because people get curious. Curious people ask questions, touch things, decide they know what things do, or decide they want them."

Naro said, "I don't want it."

"Good."

"I want to see it."

Exactly.

Tavi looked vindicated and guilty simultaneously.

"Can he?"

"No."

Naro accepted this faster than Tavi.

"Okay."

Children were strange.

Naro's sister arrived.

Maybe thirteen.

Breathing hard.

"Sorry. He runs."

"Observed."

She looked at Tavi.

"Your grown-up?"

I opened mouth.

Tavi said, "Greg."

Not answer.

Apparently enough.

She nodded.

"I'm Mevi."

"Greg."

"I know."

Of course.

Senna's portrait continued doing social damage.

Or Tavi.

Hard to know.

Mevi said, "They're saying last big run before midday."

"Source?"

She pointed at old river man.

Good source.

We stayed.

Naro and Tavi argued taxonomy.

Naro called them spillwings.

Tavi called them flying fish.

Mevi called them river kites.

Old man called them kites.

I refused vote.

Last big run came with less warning.

Water flashed.

Workers called lane clear.

Children down.

This time Tavi moved back before I told him.

Good.

Naro did not.

Mevi caught his shirt.

Shared civilization.

Fish launched.

Dozens again.

One came directly toward viewing blocks.

I saw line before body.

Blue-white streak.

"Down," I said.

Tavi ducked.

Not because city worker.

Because me.

Fish passed where his head had been and struck wall behind us.

THWACK.

It dropped between my right crutch and stone.

Blue lines flared.

I did not move crutch.

Did not touch fish.

Did not panic.

Tavi stayed crouched.

Excellent.

Fish flopped once against my boot.

Right boot.

A sharp tingling ran through leather.

Not painful.

Enough.

I shifted weight carefully without trapping it.

"Back," I said.

Tavi backed.

Worker was already coming with net.

Fish flashed again.

For half second I felt hair on right forearm lift.

Field discharge.

Small.

Worker netted it.

Released it.

Gone.

Tavi stared at me.

"It hit you."

"Boot."

"Did it shock?"

"A little."

"Are you okay?"

"Yes."

Worker asked same.

"Any numbness?"

"No. Tingling through boot. Gone."

"Good. Sit if it comes back."

"I will."

No heroic immunity.

No secret S-class body nonsense.

Just fish.

Tavi looked at water.

Then at me.

"I ducked."

"You did."

"Before it hit."

"Yes."

"Because you said."

"Also yes."

He smiled.

That mattered to him more than fish hitting me.

Maybe because being competent inside exciting thing felt good.

I understood.

We stayed another ten minutes.

No tingling returned.

Then I said, "Home."

Tavi said, "One more."

"No."

"Small one."

"No."

"What if biggest one comes after we leave?"

"Then it will live without us."

He hated this concept.

Still came.

Naro shouted goodbye.

Mevi shouted, "Walk."

Naro walked for approximately six feet.

Good luck to her.

On route home Tavi was quieter.

Not silent.

Impossible.

But thoughts had moved inward.

After three streets he said, "I shouldn't tell Naro about chest."

"Correct."

"I didn't."

"Correct."

"But I told oval."

"Yes."

"Are you mad?"

There it was.

"No."

He looked up.

"Why?"

"Because you didn't know rule. Now you do."

"What if I forget?"

"Then I remind you and become annoying."

"You're already annoying."

"Training complete."

He smiled.

"Can I tell people you got hit by fish?"

I considered.

"Yes."

"Can I say it almost killed you?"

"No."

"Can I say huge fish?"

"It was normal size."

"Huge normal fish."

"No."

"Big fish."

"Medium."

"Flying fish hit Greg."

"Accurate."

He nodded.

Truth negotiated.

At home Cala took one look at us.

"Good?"

Tavi exploded.

Words everywhere.

Fish.

Man hair.

Net.

Naro.

Big run.

Greg boot.

He reenacted ducking three times.

Lyssa was laughing before he finished.

Cala looked at me.

"Boot?"

"Minor field tingle. Gone. Worker checked. No numbness."

"Good."

Then Tavi said, "And I told Naro about oval but not chest and now rule is don't tell people what is in chest because curious people touch things and ask and want them."

Cala looked at me again.

"Good."

Not at him.

At me.

Maybe because I had not turned it into punishment.

Lyssa said, "You told Naro about Greg's oval?"

Tavi's shoulders rose.

"Before."

"He knows now," I said.

Lyssa looked at him.

"Okay."

Shoulders lowered.

Done.

No family tribunal.

Good.

Tavi spent rest of afternoon drawing fish.

Badly.

Senna had nothing to fear.

Every fish had wings.

Actual wings.

One breathed fire.

"Did that happen?" I asked.

"No."

"Then why?"

"Better."

Art.

I went upstairs before dinner.

Eight copper.

Twenty-six Vale.

No money movement.

No magic practice.

No artifact use.

I opened notes.

Almost wrote about river kites.

Stopped.

I did not need mechanism notes for everything beautiful.

That was growth.

Probably temporary.

Instead I wrote:

TAVI / PUBLIC OBJECT RULE

DON'T EXPLAIN HOUSE ARTIFACTS TO PEOPLE WITHOUT ASKING

VISIBLE DOES NOT MEAN DEMONSTRATION

Then below:

LOWER SPILL

IF KITE LANDS: DON'T TOUCH / CALL WORKER

That one was practical.

I considered title of day.

Not collector.

Not expert.

Not model.

Not even babysitter, exactly.

I had watched water.

Watched fish.

Mostly watched one small person whose feet kept trying to become somebody else's problem.

Lookout.

Temporary.

Again.

Downstairs Tavi yelled, "GREG, FISH HAS SWORD NOW."

I closed notebook.

Somebody had to supervise art.

# CHAPTER 363

## THE NEIGHBOR

The first time I used Three Reeds for something useful, it was because somebody else's kitchen had moths.

Not normal moths.

That would have been too easy.

These were on fire.

Not very much on fire.

Carrow remained committed to distinctions.

I was upstairs when somebody hammered on front door hard enough that Cala shouted, "Greg," before knowing who it was.

This felt unfair.

I went down.

Lyssa was already at door.

She opened it.

Mevi stood outside breathing hard.

Naro's older sister.

Thirteen, approximately.

Competent mostly because somebody had assigned her Naro.

"Sorry," she said. "Do you have a dark lamp?"

I looked at Lyssa.

Lyssa looked at me.

"A what?" I asked.

"Naro said Tavi said you have something that makes light not go places."

Excellent.

Household artifact privacy rule had survived less than one day.

Tavi appeared behind Cala.

"I didn't tell her."

Mevi said, "Naro did."

Tavi pointed at her like this proved innocence in court.

"See?"

Technically.

I looked back at Mevi.

"Why do you need one?"

"Emberwings."

"What are emberwings?"

Everyone except me knew again.

Lyssa said, "Red moths."

"That catch fire?"

"Glow."

Mevi said, "They burn paper if enough land together."

Better.

"Where?"

"Our kitchen. Mama's at late wash. Naro opened west shutter because smoke and now they're all at window and two got in and I closed it but the lamp is pulling more to glass and I don't want to put lamp out because Mama said don't leave us dark after blue hour last week and I know blue hour isn't tonight but she said don't, and Naro keeps trying to hit them with towel, and I told him stop because if he breaks lamp then we have moths and broken lamp and dark, which seems worse."

There.

Mevi did not speak like Greg.

She spoke like a thirteen-year-old who had been solving one escalating household problem for too long.

"Are the two inside still inside?"

"One went back out. One is on ceiling."

"Anything burning?"

"No."

"Smoke?"

"Cooking smoke."

"People hurt?"

"No."

Good.

Cala said, "Their mother is Hessa?"

I turned.

"What?"

Mevi shook head.

"Hessa is auntie. Mama is Dera."

Different Hessa problem avoided.

Cala nodded like she had known this and simply asked badly.

"Dera at wash house?"

"South wash."

"When back?"

"After late bell."

Cala looked at me.

Not order.

Question.

I looked toward back room.

Three Reeds.

Established effect.

Directional stray-light reduction.

One controlled lamp context.

No rated pairing here.

But importantly, it had worked while physically separate from theatre lamp.

We did not need to attach it to their lamp.

Could keep vents clear.

Short activation.

Permission.

Real objective.

Reduce light leaking toward west window while preserving enough kitchen light to keep children out of dark.

Maybe.

I said, "I have something that might reduce spill toward window. It doesn't make darkness. It isn't rated for your lamp. We keep it separate. If it heats too much or does anything weird, off."

Mevi nodded immediately.

"Fine."

"You understand that was not a promise it works?"

"Greg."

Lyssa smiled.

Apparently my reputation had spread two doors east.

"Fine."

I held hand toward Cala.

She already had key.

Of course.

"You want me there?" Lyssa asked.

"No."

"Good."

Honest relationship.

Tavi said, "I can go."

Three adults said, "No."

He looked betrayed.

Mevi said, "Naro is already there."

Tavi brightened.

"Then I should."

"That made argument worse," I said.

I unlocked cedar chest.

Three Reeds sat upright where I had left it.

Ugly brass.

Four square glass sides.

Cloudy rear stone.

Front shutter.

Family card protected above.

I took unit and card.

Did not take holdfast.

Did not take rest stone.

Relocked chest.

Returned key to Cala.

Wrapped Three Reeds loosely for carrying, vents irrelevant while inactive but habits were cheap.

Shoulder bag was awkward with it.

Still workable.

Oval remained in bag because oval lived there.

No reason to remove it.

Tavi followed us to door.

"Can I watch from our window?"

"No," Cala said.

"Why?"

"Because then you open our shutter and invite moths here."

He considered.

"Small opening?"

"No."

"Crack?"

"No."

"Door?"

"Tavi."

He stopped.

Mevi and I walked two doors east.

Very far journey.

I had passed house dozens of times.

Never entered.

That was city too.

You could know someone's wall before their name.

Their front door was green once, now mostly gray where paint had surrendered.

Mevi opened it.

"Naro, I got Greg."

From kitchen:

"Does he have it?"

"Don't yell."

"Does he?"

"Yes."

I said, "Maybe."

Naro yelled, "Maybe!"

Mevi closed eyes.

"Sorry."

"I know Tavi."

This was enough.

Kitchen was back room with west window above wash basin.

One oil lamp sat on center table.

Ordinary flame behind glass chimney.

No obvious magical assembly.

Good.

Three Reeds did not touch it.

West shutter closed.

Window glass looked alive.

At least twenty emberwings clung outside.

Maybe thirty.

Bodies dark red.

Wings thin as burned paper.

Each carried faint orange line along wing edge.

When they shifted, lines brightened.

Not fire exactly.

Heat enough to matter, apparently.

One inside clung to ceiling beam.

Naro stood beneath it holding rolled towel.

"Put that down," Mevi said.

"I'm not hitting."

"Then why hold it?"

"Ready."

"For what?"

"If it flies at me."

"Then duck."

"What if it chases?"

"Moths don't chase."

I looked at emberwing.

"Do these?"

Mevi hesitated.

"I don't think so."

Naro looked vindicated.

Excellent.

I set wrapped unit on counter away from lamp.

"Before anything, where do you normally put lamp?"

"There," Mevi said.

"And moths normally?"

"Some. Not like this."

"Why tonight?"

"Warm rain."

River kites in morning.

Emberwings at night.

Carrow had seasonal scheduling.

"Window was open?"

"Naro opened shutter and glass panel because smoke."

Naro said, "Pot smoked."

Mevi said, "Because you put peel in flame."

"By accident."

"You threw it."

"I missed bucket."

"With peel."

"Yes."

I did not need full case history.

"Window closed now."

"Yes."

"Lamp has to stay lit."

"Mama said."

"Fine."

I looked at room.

Main light needed table, stove edge, path to door.

Window did not need light.

Three Reeds had reduced stray wash beyond faced direction while main lit region mostly stayed.

If same broad behavior held here, face it toward window from between lamp and west wall, not touching lamp.

But theatre lamp context had been current-rated magical carried service.

This was oil flame.

Different source.

Could do nothing.

Could behave differently.

We were not testing for curiosity.

We were attempting bounded use because current problem existed.

Difference mattered.

"I need clear table corner," I said.

Mevi moved bowl.

Naro moved spoon.

Then moved spoon back because apparently bowl had been covering spoon territory.

I unwrapped unit.

Naro leaned.

"That's it?"

"Yes."

"It looks like bad lantern."

"Excellent assessment."

"Tavi said stone."

"Different thing."

His eyes widened.

Privacy rule doing excellent work.

Mevi said, "Naro."

"I'm not asking."

He was absolutely asking with face.

I placed Three Reeds on table corner, several feet from oil lamp, faced front toward west window.

Vents clear.

Nothing touching rear plate.

No paper nearby.

Family card stayed folded in my bag, not beside flame.

"Nobody touches it," I said.

Naro said, "I know."

"You especially."

"Why especially?"

Mevi said, "Because you held towel at moth."

Fair evidence.

I checked room once more.

No reason to move shutter.

No reason to alter lamp.

Baseline was obvious enough.

Lamp threw warm oval across table, stove, west wall, window glass.

Outside emberwings clustered brightest near center of lit pane.

I pressed rear stud.

Cloudy stone warmed faintly under thumb.

No emitted light.

One breath.

Two.

Nothing.

Three.

West wall changed.

Subtle.

Not dark.

The warm lamp wash near window flattened.

Edges dulled.

Glass lost some amber glow while table remained readable.

Mevi whispered, "Oh."

Naro said, "It ate light."

"No."

"It did."

"It reduced spill."

"Ate spill."

"Worse."

Outside moths did not immediately leave.

Important.

They were already there.

One moved.

Then another.

Orange edges brightened.

A few shifted toward upper pane where kitchen light remained stronger.

Could be response.

Could be moths.

We waited.

Not laboratory wait.

Practical wait.

Would window stop attracting more?

Would current cluster disperse?

Would Three Reeds heat too much?

I put fingers near rear plate without touching hot surface directly.

Warm increasing.

Expected from prior use.

"How long can it stay on?" Mevi asked.

"I don't know."

She looked at me.

"Then why is it on?"

Good question.

"Short use only. We know it warms quickly. We don't know safe duration. I'm watching heat."

"How short?"

"Shorter than finding out badly."

She frowned.

Not satisfied.

Correctly.

"Five minutes?"

"I don't know."

"Two?"

"I don't know."

"Then turn it off when hot?"

"Before hot."

"How do you know before?"

"By checking."

"That sounds like guessing."

"It is bounded guessing with exit condition."

Naro said, "That's still guessing."

Children destroyed professional vocabulary.

"Yes."

Mevi nodded.

Better.

The inside emberwing lifted from beam.

Naro raised hands.

Mevi grabbed towel before he could.

Moth crossed room.

Its orange edges brightened near lamp.

Then it turned toward window.

Closed glass.

Bad.

It fluttered along pane.

Could not exit.

"Can we open it?" Naro asked.

Opening window meant outside cluster.

No.

Mevi said, "Back door."

Good.

She opened kitchen-to-yard door.

Dark outside.

Moth did not care.

Still at window.

"Lamp," she said.

"What?"

"Move lamp toward door?"

I looked.

Maybe.

But moving open flame around children and artifact to manipulate insect was more intervention than needed.

"Could put out lamp for ten seconds," I said.

Mevi shook head immediately.

"Mama said lamp stays."

There.

Constraint.

Not mine to override casually.

"Then leave door open and wait."

We waited.

Moth eventually left window.

Crossed ceiling.

Naro ducked despite it being six feet above him.

Good training.

It passed through back door.

Mevi shut door.

"One problem gone."

"One," I said.

Outside west window, cluster had thinned.

Not dramatically.

Maybe twenty down to twelve.

No new dense arrivals that I saw.

Could be Three Reeds.

Could be run changing.

We did not need proof tonight.

We needed fewer hot moths at kitchen glass.

Rear plate now distinctly warm.

Not painful.

Enough.

I switched unit off.

Within breaths, west-wall lamp spill strengthened again.

Glass amber returned.

That was useful confirmation of light effect here.

Not moth causation.

Naro said, "Turn it back on."

"Cooling."

"But moths."

"Still outside."

"More will come."

"Maybe."

Mevi looked at window.

Then at lamp.

"Can we block light without magic?"

Excellent.

I looked around.

"Curtain?"

"Mama took it down because emberwings burned holes last year."

Reasonable.

"Board?"

"Shutter is outside glass."

Right.

Window design bad for internal light blocking.

A baking tray leaned by stove.

Metal.

Large enough to cover lower pane from inside if propped safely.

"That yours?"

"Yes."

"Can it stand in basin?"

Mevi tried.

Tray fit diagonally between basin lip and window frame, covering maybe two-thirds of lit glass without touching flame or moths.

Kitchen light remained.

Window glow reduced more crudely than Three Reeds.

Naro said, "Magic tray."

"Correct."

Mevi laughed.

Good.

We left tray.

Three Reeds cooled on clear counter.

Moth numbers outside did not increase during next several minutes.

Maybe run was passing.

Maybe reduced glow helped.

Didn't matter enough to claim.

"So we don't need it?" Mevi asked.

I looked at Three Reeds.

"Maybe not now."

That felt good.

Artifact had helped us discover direction of problem.

Then ordinary tray solved ongoing need without uncertain heat.

Not every use needed maximum magic.

Naro looked disappointed.

"But dark lamp worked."

"Shade unit."

"Dark lamp."

"No."

"It made dark."

"Less light."

"Dark."

I gave up.

Mevi said, "Can it stay here until Mama comes?"

There.

Ownership problem.

"No."

She nodded immediately.

No offense.

"Because dangerous?"

"Because I don't know safe duration, you don't know controls, and it's mine."

"Okay."

Naro said, "I know controls."

"You watched one button."

"That's controls."

"This is why no."

Mevi pointed at him.

"Also that."

We waited until Three Reeds was cool enough to wrap.

I did not reactivate.

Before leaving I checked window.

Six emberwings.

Then five.

One lifted and vanished into night.

Good enough.

"If they come back heavy?" Mevi asked.

"Keep window closed. Keep tray blocking lamp spill if it stays stable and away from flame. Don't hit moths. If one gets in, back door and patience. If anything starts actually burning, leave and get adult or fire bell."

"Fire bell is at corner."

"Good."

"I know."

Of course she did.

"Your mother back after late bell?"

"Yes."

"If you want, I can tell Cala you're alone until then."

Mevi's face changed slightly.

Pride.

Annoyance.

Relief she did not want named.

"She knows Mama works wash."

"Then fine."

I did not turn neighbor help into supervision takeover.

At door Naro said, "Tell Tavi it worked."

"You can tell him."

"He'll say I touched it."

"Did you?"

"No."

"Then say that too."

"Can I say it saved house?"

"No."

"Saved window?"

"No."

"Saved moths?"

"No."

"What did it save?"

"Approximately one baking tray from unemployment."

He stared.

Mevi laughed again.

I went home.

Two doors.

Tavi was waiting behind front door like informant.

"Did it work?"

"Some."

"What happened?"

"Moths."

"I know moths."

"Then why ask?"

He made frustrated noise.

Lyssa said from table, "Don't torture him."

"Professional development."

Cala looked at wrapped unit.

"Hot?"

"Cool now. Warmed during short use."

"Anything bad?"

"No."

I explained.

Oil lamp.

West window.

Separate unit.

Directional reduction worked in second light context.

Moth cluster thinned but causation uncertain.

Inside moth exited through back door.

Baking tray became better long-duration solution.

Lyssa said, "So you took five-copper magic lantern two doors over and solved problem with pan."

"Tray."

"Important."

"Very."

Tavi said, "Can I see emberwings?"

Three adults said, "No."

Tradition.

I asked Cala for key.

She gave it.

I unwrapped Three Reeds.

Checked rear plate cool.

No smell.

No discoloration.

No visible change.

Stored upright in cedar chest.

Family card above.

Relocked.

Returned key.

Done.

No second test.

No shutter experiments.

No trying household lamps because now we knew it did something with oil light too.

That knowledge could sit.

It was allowed.

After dinner Dera knocked.

I knew it was her because Mevi stood beside her and Naro stood behind both pretending not to be involved.

Dera was broad-faced, tired, sleeves still damp from wash house.

"You helped my kids."

Statement.

"A little."

"Mevi said you brought some shade thing."

"Yes."

"She said you wouldn't leave it."

"Correct."

Dera nodded.

"Good."

Interesting.

"She handled it well," I said.

Mevi looked away.

Dera did not.

"She usually does. Too much."

That sentence carried more house than I owned.

I left it alone.

Dera held out small paper packet.

"Wash soap. Good kind. Take it."

I looked at packet.

"You don't owe me."

"I know."

Different.

Gift.

Neighbor exchange.

Not payment.

I took it.

"Thank you."

"Thank you."

Naro said, "It saved tray."

Dera looked at him.

"What?"

Mevi covered face.

I said, "Long story."

Dera sighed.

"Everything with him is."

Naro looked proud.

They left.

Cala inspected soap.

"Good kind."

"Apparently."

"It is."

She put it near wash shelf.

Not mine anymore.

Household absorbed benefit without ledger.

I went upstairs.

Eight copper.

Twenty-six Vale.

No bit accounting.

No mana.

One practical artifact use.

I opened notes.

This time I did write artifact result because unlike fish, this was mine and heat could hurt somebody.

THREE REEDS

SECOND LIGHT CONTEXT: ORDINARY OIL LAMP / SEPARATE / UNMOUNTED

FACED WEST WINDOW

VISIBLE STRAY WASH AT WINDOW REDUCED

MAIN TABLE LIGHT REMAINED USABLE

OFF = WINDOW SPILL RETURNED

REAR PLATE WARMED QUICKLY AGAIN

NO SAFE DURATION ESTABLISHED

EMBERWINGS THINNED DURING USE / DO NOT CLAIM CAUSE

Then:

PRACTICAL ANSWER WAS BAKING TRAY.

I stared at that.

Five copper artifact defeated by kitchen equipment.

No.

Wrong frame.

Artifact had done exactly what I bought it hoping it might do.

It controlled where light leaked.

That mattered.

Then a cheaper object was enough for this specific problem.

Also mattered.

Owning tool did not mean every problem deserved tool.

I almost wrote that.

Too wise again.

Instead:

DON'T LEAVE ARTIFACT WITH CHILDREN.

Better.

I closed notebook.

From two doors east, somebody shouted.

Naro, probably.

Then Mevi shouted back.

Normal.

I had not been an expert tonight.

Had not been technician.

Had not been hero.

I had walked two doors because somebody knocked.

Brought something useful.

Stayed until problem became ordinary again.

Neighbor.

That one did not feel temporary in quite the same way.

# CHAPTER 364

## THE DEBTOR

Vale found me through bread.

Not metaphorically.

A folded gray slip sat under loaf Cala brought home from corner baker, which meant somebody had handed it to baker, who had handed it to Cala, who had put bread directly on top of my financial obligations.

Efficient.

"Your debt has become breakfast," Lyssa said.

"Finally useful."

Cala cut loaf.

"Read it before butter gets on it."

I pulled slip free.

Vale office mark.

Not review notice.

Different corner stamp.

I opened it.

AVAILABLE SERVICE CREDIT

EAST LEDGER ANNEX

TOMORROW / THIRD BELL

DOCUMENT TRANSFER ASSISTANCE

SEATED / TWO HOURS EXPECTED

CREDIT: 3 C PRINCIPAL-EQUIVALENT

REFUSABLE

CONFIRM BY OFFICE CLOSE TODAY

I read it twice.

Lyssa said, "Three?"

"Three."

"For sitting?"

"Apparently my strongest profession."

Cala put butter on bread.

"What kind of documents?"

"Transfer assistance."

"That means nothing."

"It means documents will be transferred."

"Greg."

"I don't know."

That part mattered.

Vale debt sat at twenty-six copper principal-equivalent.

Three credit would make twenty-three.

No cash in hand.

No bits.

No purchase.

Just less future obligation.

I had eight copper private cash.

Enough that three copper of debt reduction no longer felt same as three copper of survival.

Still real.

Very real.

Lyssa leaned across table.

"You going?"

"Probably."

"Why probably?"

"Because I want to know what transfer assistance means before I agree."

"Slip says confirm."

"Office exists."

She smiled slightly.

"Look at you."

"What?"

"Reading terms before accepting money."

"Credit."

"Worse."

Cala said, "Eat first."

This household had priorities.

After breakfast I walked to Vale office.

Not because three copper needed emotional ceremony.

Because service credit came from debt agreement, and debt agreements became dangerous when words blurred.

Vale's front room looked exactly as it had during review.

Two benches.

Three writing desks behind waist-high rail.

Posted rate board.

Posted service notices.

One woman arguing quietly about whether a delivery counted as late if receiving office closed early.

A clerk I had not met took slip.

"Name?"

"Greg."

"Account mark?"

I gave it.

She checked ledger.

"Yes. East Annex tomorrow. Third bell."

"What is document transfer assistance?"

She looked at me.

Not annoyed.

Assessing how much explanation I needed.

"Old secured accounts are being moved from annex paper shelves into consolidated registry ledgers. Staff reads account marks and page references. Assistant confirms physical bundle labels against transfer sheet, keeps page stacks in order, and marks missing or damaged leaves for clerk review."

"Do I interpret account terms?"

"No."

"Copy balances?"

"No."

"Handle money?"

"No."

"Read private account contents?"

"Only cover marks and page numbers unless clerk directs otherwise. You are not authorized for terms."

Good.

"Why me?"

She checked ledger again.

"Your account allows service credit. Annex requested people who can read clearly and remain seated."

I waited.

She added, "You have completed office service before without dispute."

There.

Not special.

Known enough.

"Three principal-equivalent for expected two hours?"

"Three if completed."

"If it runs longer?"

"You may stop at two hours unless you agree extension. Extension rate would be stated before extension."

"If they end early?"

"Full three if supervisor releases you as complete."

"If I leave early?"

"No credit unless supervisor records completed scope."

Reasonable.

"Does accepting change next review?"

"No."

"Any obligation to accept future service?"

"No."

"Does refusal affect account standing?"

"No."

"Does completion reduce principal-equivalent from twenty-six to twenty-three?"

She checked.

"If no other account event posts before then, yes."

Correct answer.

Not promise against universe.

"I'll take it."

She marked slip.

"Bring this. East Annex, not main office. Third bell."

"Where?"

She gave directions.

I left.

No drama.

No hidden trap.

Debt could simply be work.

That was almost more unsettling.

I spent rest of day not preparing for paper.

This required discipline.

Tavi asked what job was.

"Looking at numbers."

"You do that at home free."

"I have been exploited."

Cala said, "By yourself."

"Worst employer."

Lyssa was trimming thread ends from a finished cuff.

"Three off Vale?"

"If completed."

"Twenty-three after?"

"If nothing else posts."

She looked at me.

"You went to ask that exact sentence, didn't you?"

"Yes."

"Good."

No joke.

Just good.

That landed.

Next morning I went East Annex.

No artifacts beyond oval in normal shoulder bag.

No Three Reeds.

No holdfast.

No rest stone.

Eight copper stayed home except ordinary carry coin I did not count because bit continuity remained a problem nobody in world knew existed.

I disliked that sentence immediately.

Not because of money.

Because I had started thinking like ledger.

East Annex sat behind old court buildings where streets narrowed and walls became older than signs attached to them.

Three-story stone building.

Small windows.

Iron shutters open for morning.

Vale mark over door, older style than main office.

Inside smelled like paper, dust, wax, and old damp that had lost argument with limewash but not memory.

A man at front table took my marked slip.

Late forties maybe.

Thin hair.

Sleeves rolled exactly same height.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"I'm Orsen. Transfer clerk."

Role supplied.

"Sit."

I sat.

Good beginning.

Table had three shallow wooden trays.

LEFT SHELF

TRANSFER

HOLD

A stack of narrow gray sheets.

A pencil.

A small square stamp with no ink pad because stamp itself held blue marking stone in base.

Magical office equipment.

Naturally.

Orsen pointed.

"You don't use stamp."

"Good."

"You read bundle cover. Read transfer line. If account mark and page range match, move bundle from left tray to transfer tray. Keep sequence. I stamp after."

"If mismatch?"

"Hold tray. Say mismatch."

"Missing page?"

"Hold."

"Damaged?"

"Hold."

"Unreadable?"

"Hold."

"If I think I know why?"

He looked at me.

"Do you?"

"No."

"Then hold."

I liked Orsen.

Not personally yet.

Professionally.

He handed first transfer sheet.

Account mark.

Shelf code.

Page range.

Nothing about debt terms.

Nothing about names except abbreviated holder initials on some bundle covers.

Private enough to matter.

Boring enough to protect.

First bundle:

E-14-77

pages 1 through 6.

Transfer line:

E-14-77

1-6.

I moved it.

Orsen stamped transfer line.

Blue mark flashed once, then went dull.

Next.

E-14-81.

Pages 1-4.

Match.

Transfer.

Stamp.

Third.

E-14-83.

Pages 1-9.

Cover said 1-8.

"Mismatch."

Orsen stopped stamp.

"What?"

"Transfer says one through nine. Bundle cover says one through eight."

He took bundle.

Did not open.

Placed HOLD.

"Good."

No investigation.

Next.

Paper work had rhythm.

Not exciting rhythm.

But satisfying.

Read.

Compare.

Move.

Pause.

Stamp.

Blue flash.

The magical stamp was probably some registry-authentication device.

I did not ask.

Growth again.

At maybe twentieth bundle, Orsen said, "You can ask."

I looked at him.

"What?"

"Stamp. You've looked at it twelve times."

Damn.

"What does it do?"

"Marks transfer clerk, office, date block. Harder to alter than ink."

"How?"

"Don't know."

Perfect.

"Who services it?"

"Registry instruments."

"How often?"

He stared.

I stopped.

"Sorry."

"Quarterly unless it starts ghosting."

"Ghosting?"

"Double mark."

"Cause?"

"Greg."

"Stopping."

He almost smiled.

Dialogue victory.

An hour passed.

My back wanted different chair.

I shifted.

Orsen noticed.

"Break?"

"Two minutes."

He nodded.

I stood.

Hands stretched.

Right leg loosened.

No issue.

Across room two other clerks worked with larger ledgers.

One woman had tiny brass page-weight that crawled itself half inch whenever she turned page.

I looked once.

Then away.

Not everything magical needed me.

This city was becoming increasingly inconsiderate about that.

I sat again.

Second hour started with damaged bundle.

Not dramatic.

Water stain along lower edge.

Cover page readable.

Page range maybe complete.

"Damage," I said.

Hold.

Then three matches.

Then one shelf code mismatch.

Hold.

Then a bundle with loose thread binding.

"Damage?"

Orsen looked.

"Hold."

"Even pages intact?"

"Binding is part of transfer condition."

Good.

I stopped trying to infer categories and just used rule.

Halfway through second hour door opened.

A woman entered carrying a red corded packet.

Not Vale clerk.

Clothes too expensive for archive dust.

Dark blue outer coat.

Silver buttons.

No obvious artifact.

She spoke to front clerk quietly.

Front clerk pointed toward Orsen.

She came over.

Orsen's posture changed.

Not fear.

Formality.

"Mistress Ceral."

"Orsen. I need transfer copy for Morrow account block."

"Which mark?"

She gave one.

I kept eyes on my sheet.

Private account.

Not mine.

Orsen checked shelf list.

"That block is in today's transfer."

"Then I need it before transfer closes."

"If it has moved, request through consolidated desk after stamp."

"I need it now."

"If it has not moved, I can route request. If it has moved, consolidated desk."

Her voice tightened.

"Orsen."

His did not.

"Mistress Ceral."

Same words.

Different war.

I looked at next bundle.

M-22-11.

Not Morrow maybe.

Could be.

Didn't matter.

Transfer sheet matched.

I moved it.

Orsen stamped.

Ceral noticed me.

"Who is this?"

Orsen said, "Service assistant."

There.

Role within role.

She looked at my tray.

"Does he have access to account papers?"

"Cover marks and page ranges only."

"He can read names."

"Initials where present."

"That is still access."

Orsen said, "Authorized under transfer protocol."

She looked at me.

I said nothing.

This was not my argument.

Her gaze dropped to crutches.

Then back to face.

No comment.

Good.

"Which bundles have moved?" she asked.

Orsen said, "I cannot disclose other account transfer status to you. Give front clerk your mark and request."

"I gave you mark."

"Give front clerk."

She stood still.

Then turned and left table.

No explosion.

No villain reveal.

Just person accustomed to access meeting procedure.

I waited until she was out of earshot.

"Do I need to know who that was?"

"No."

"Excellent."

Orsen stamped next line.

We continued.

Ten minutes later front clerk brought a request slip.

Orsen checked it.

Looked at HOLD tray.

One bundle there matched requested account mark.

Interesting.

He took it.

Not to Ceral.

To another clerk.

"Condition hold before request."

Other clerk nodded.

That was all.

I did not ask why.

I did not read name.

I did not connect imaginary dots.

Debt office contained other people's lives.

Being allowed near paper did not make them mine.

We finished expected stack with maybe six minutes left.

Orsen counted transfer tray.

Checked hold slips.

Four holds.

One page-range mismatch.

One water damage.

One shelf-code mismatch.

One loose binding.

No numbers beyond that mattered.

He looked at clock.

"Done."

"Full scope?"

"Yes."

He signed my service slip.

Then stamped it.

Blue flash.

"Take main office or front desk here?"

"Front can post."

"Today?"

"Usually before close."

Usually.

Good word.

I brought slip to front.

Different clerk entered account mark.

Checked service record.

"Three principal-equivalent posted."

"Current balance?"

She looked.

"Twenty-three copper principal-equivalent."

There.

Real.

Not future.

Not maybe.

Twenty-three.

"Any other account change?"

"No."

"Next routine review still same date?"

"Yes."

She gave small stamped receipt.

No fee.

I folded it into shoulder bag with prior Vale paper.

Then walked outside.

I still had eight copper.

That was strange part.

When debt had been larger relative to everything, reducing it felt like money disappearing into hole.

Now I could see two separate things.

Cash bought options now.

Debt reduction bought fewer claims on later.

Neither was fake.

Neither was same.

I stood outside East Annex while carts passed.

For first time, twenty-three looked meaningfully smaller than twenty-six.

Not small.

Smaller.

Stair step.

Good.

I did not celebrate by spending money.

This was heroic restraint and should be recorded by historians.

Instead I went home.

Lyssa was at table.

Cala sorting dried beans.

Tavi drawing what appeared to be fish stabbing building.

Continuity.

Lyssa looked up.

"Three?"

I held up receipt.

"Posted."

"Twenty-three?"

"Twenty-three."

She smiled.

Not huge.

Enough.

"Nice."

Cala said, "Good."

Tavi said, "Can debt buy things?"

"No."

"Then why have it?"

"Excellent question."

Lyssa laughed.

Cala did not.

"Don't teach him that."

"I didn't create debt."

"You created yours."

Technically previous Greg had.

Same body.

Same legal person.

Mine enough.

"Fair."

Tavi looked between us.

"What happens when it gets zero?"

I answered before joke.

"Then Vale doesn't have that claim on me anymore."

He thought.

"So you're done?"

"With that debt."

"Then get zero."

Children remained strong strategists.

"Working on it."

Later I opened notebook.

Wrote:

VALE

SERVICE CREDIT POSTED: 3 C PRINCIPAL-EQUIVALENT

CURRENT: 23 C

CASH: 8 C

NEXT REVIEW UNCHANGED

NO FUTURE SERVICE OBLIGATION CREATED

Then stopped.

I almost wrote what job taught me.

Something about debt being time.

Something about cash and future claims.

Something very wise.

It all sounded like a man trying to turn two hours of moving paper into religion.

So I crossed blank space instead.

The role was simpler.

Debtor.

Still.

Less than yesterday.

But still.

That was fine.

Some roles did not end because chapter did.

# CHAPTER 365

## THE PARTNER

Lyssa asked me to carry cloth.

This was suspicious.

Not because cloth was dangerous.

Usually.

Because Lyssa could carry cloth herself.

"How much?" I asked.

She stood beside table with two folded bundles tied in gray cord.

"Those."

"That is not a weight."

"They're cloth, Greg."

"Armor can be cloth."

"They are not armor."

"Blankets can kill."

"Only if I use one correctly."

Cala kept peeling turnips.

Tavi looked interested.

I pointed at him.

"Witness."

"To murder?"

"Potentially."

Lyssa pushed one bundle toward me.

"Marra wants these back before midday. I have the other one and the sample book."

There.

Actual reason.

Three hands worth of objects.

She had two.

I had none, technically, because crutches occupied both.

But shoulder bag existed.

And straps.

"You want my bag?"

"I want your body."

Tavi made a noise.

Lyssa closed eyes.

I said, "Excellent phrasing."

"For carrying cloth."

"Too late."

Cala said, "Go."

Lyssa looked at her.

"He started it."

"You finished it. Go."

We went.

The bundle fit under shoulder-bag strap against my right side if tied separately with a short cord.

Not elegant.

Stable enough.

Lyssa checked knot twice.

"If it slides, tell me."

"I have survived cargo before."

"That sentence is why I said tell me."

Fair.

We took East Cloth route toward Marra's green door.

No blue hour.

No flying fish.

No bridge becoming architecture experiment.

Carrow was almost disappointingly normal.

Morning carts.

Wet stone.

A woman shouting prices for pears.

Two apprentices arguing over whether a red bolt was rust or brick.

Lyssa slowed at one shop window.

Not coat shop.

Thread.

Rows of narrow spools behind glass.

She looked maybe two seconds.

Then kept walking.

I said nothing.

This was apparently relationship growth.

At next corner she said, "You can ask."

"About thread?"

"You were visibly not asking."

"I was respecting boundaries."

"Loudly."

"Difficult skill."

She smiled.

"Marra might have larger work."

That changed route without changing street.

"Larger how?"

"Don't know."

"You know something."

"I know she sent a note yesterday asking if I could stay after delivery."

"That is something."

"Yes."

"You didn't mention it."

"I am mentioning it now."

I waited.

She waited.

This was trap.

Not malicious.

Relationship trap.

"Do you want me there?" I asked.

"I asked you to carry cloth."

"That is not answer."

"I know."

We walked another half block.

Then she said, "Maybe."

Good.

Messy answer.

"For what?"

"I don't know yet. If she offers something, I might want another person hearing terms. Not deciding. Hearing."

There.

Role arrived before title.

"You want witness."

"Maybe."

"Not adviser?"

She gave me look.

"Especially not adviser unless I ask."

"Cruel."

"You survived."

"Barely."

"And if I don't want you in room, you leave."

"Yes."

She glanced over.

Maybe checking whether yes had hidden argument.

It didn't.

"Okay," she said.

Marra's green door was open.

Inside smelled like wool steam, chalk, lamp oil, and hot iron.

Three worktables occupied front room.

One woman I recognized from prior visit sat pinning dark sleeve to form.

Jessa was near back shelves with mouth full of thread.

She saw us.

Pulled thread free.

"Late."

Lyssa looked at wall clock.

"We're early."

"Then I was emotionally prepared too soon."

Jessa's speech remained hers.

Good.

Marra came from back room carrying ledger and two cloth cards.

"Lyssa."

"Marra."

"Greg."

"Furniture."

Marra looked at bundle tied to me.

"Useful furniture."

"Rare."

Lyssa untied it before conversation could become species.

We set both bundles on table.

Marra opened first.

Gray-blue work cloth.

Checked edges.

Second bundle, heavier brown weave.

She rubbed thumb across seam sample Lyssa had pinned to top.

"Good."

Lyssa said, "The brown pulls if you turn seam too tight."

"I saw."

"Then why did you mark same allowance?"

Marra looked at her.

"Because I wanted to see if you would follow mark or cloth."

Lyssa went still.

Not angry exactly.

I stayed furniture.

"That's stupid," Lyssa said.

Jessa, from shelf, said, "It is a little stupid."

Marra ignored her.

"Why?"

"Because if you want to know whether I can judge cloth, ask me to judge cloth. Don't put bad instruction on paid work and wait to see whether I catch it."

Marra set sample down.

"You changed it."

"Yes."

"Correctly."

"That's not point."

There was history here I did not own.

Maybe apprenticeship culture.

Maybe Marra's style.

Maybe Lyssa hated tests disguised as work.

I knew which side I liked.

Did not matter.

Not my argument.

Marra said, "Fair."

Lyssa blinked.

So did I, internally.

Marra continued, "I should have told you it was judgment sample."

Lyssa's shoulders lowered half inch.

"Yes."

"Next time I will."

Jessa said, "Write date down."

Marra looked at her.

"Shelf."

"I'm shelving."

She was not.

She started.

Marra turned ledger around.

"Sit."

Lyssa sat.

I remained standing until she looked at me.

"You too, if you're staying."

I looked at Lyssa.

She nodded.

I sat.

Partner.

Not business partner.

Not yet anything contractual.

Just person invited to stay.

Marra opened ledger to marked page.

"I have a six-week house order."

Lyssa did not move.

Marra pointed.

"South Hall kitchens replacing service aprons, sleeve guards, carry wraps, and heat-cloths. Not uniforms. Work goods. They want first delivery in ten days, then weekly lots."

"How much?" Lyssa asked.

"Enough that I cannot do all finishing here without dropping regular work."

"How much for me?"

Good question.

Marra slid one cloth card forward.

Not money yet.

Scope.

"First lot: twelve aprons, twelve sleeve pairs, eight carry wraps. Cut here. You take finishing home. Return in four days. If accepted, second lot larger."

Lyssa read card.

"Heat-cloths?"

"Not first lot."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"I don't know your edge treatment for them."

"Good."

This time not test.

Just answer.

Lyssa read again.

"Materials supplied?"

"Cloth, thread, fasteners supplied. You supply needles, shears, pressing setup."

"Pattern?"

"Cut pieces here. Marked."

"Corrections?"

"My error, I pay rework. Your seam error, you rework. Material defect, bring back, don't substitute."

"Rejected finish?"

"Specific reason written."

"How much?"

Marra finally turned second card.

FIRST LOT

6 C

ON ACCEPTED RETURN

Lyssa went quiet.

Six copper.

Not enormous.

Not trivial.

For four days home finishing around other life.

Potential second lot larger, unknown.

I kept face neutral.

This was her money.

Her work.

Her decision.

Marra looked at me.

"You can breathe."

"I am."

"Very carefully."

Lyssa said, "He does that when trying not to have opinion."

"I have many opinions."

"Keep them."

"Heroically."

Marra pushed card closer.

"You can take until tomorrow morning."

Lyssa asked, "Why me?"

Marra leaned back.

Not short answer apparently.

"Because you finish clean. Because you notice cloth before mark. Because when you are late, you tell me before I have to find you. Because you have enough pride to redo bad work and enough sense to complain about it first. Because Jessa is already full and because I am not paying shop rate for work I don't need done in shop."

Jessa called from shelf, "Also she is less annoying than Fera."

Marra said, "That too."

Lyssa looked at card.

"Six."

"Six."

"For first lot."

"Accepted return."

"And second?"

"If first passes, likely eight to ten depending quantity. I won't promise before Hall confirms weekly count."

Good.

No imaginary future income.

Lyssa tapped card.

"Can I do first here instead?"

Marra's eyebrows rose.

"Why?"

"I want to see pace before I bring twelve aprons into Cala's house and discover I hate everyone."

I looked at her.

She looked at me.

"Everyone includes you."

"I assumed."

Marra considered.

"Bench space costs me."

"How much?"

"One copper off lot if you use rear bench all four days."

Lyssa made face.

"No."

"Then home."

"What about one day?"

"Two bits."

There it was.

Bits.

My continuity headache returned from dead.

Not Greg's money.

Lyssa's.

Safe.

I nearly laughed.

No one asked why.

Lyssa said, "One day rear bench. I finish first four aprons and sleeve pairs here, learn pace, take rest home. Two bits off six copper?"

"Two bits bench fee, paid today. Six copper still on accepted return."

Important distinction.

Lyssa frowned.

"Why not deduct?"

"Separate books."

"Of course."

Marra shrugged.

"I didn't invent books."

"Someone did. I dislike them."

I said, "Strong position."

Lyssa looked at me.

"Furniture."

"Silent furniture."

Marra smiled.

Lyssa pulled small purse from coat pocket.

Charcoal coat.

Deep pocket.

Useful already.

She counted two bits.

Her bits.

Not mine.

Paid Marra.

Marra wrote bench use on card.

"Today?"

"Today."

"You brought shears?"

Lyssa held up sample book.

"No."

Marra looked at her.

Lyssa sighed.

"I thought we were talking."

"We are. Work can start after midday if you go get them."

"I can."

She looked at me.

There it was again.

Not request yet.

I waited.

"Can you bring my shears and needle roll back?"

"Yes."

"They're in upper room, left drawer of work chest. Needle roll tied yellow. Not blue."

"Anything else?"

"Small pressing cloth. Hanging behind stove."

"Anything else?"

She thought.

"Food."

"For you?"

"Unless you plan to eat it on route."

"I hadn't until accusation."

Marra said, "Bring enough. She gets mean when hungry."

Lyssa looked at her.

"You offered me work."

"I contain contradictions."

I stood.

"I can return before midday."

Lyssa caught my sleeve lightly before I moved.

"Wait."

I waited.

She looked at Marra.

"Can he hear one more thing?"

Marra said, "Your choice."

Lyssa looked back at me.

"Stay."

I sat again.

Marra turned ledger page.

"House order has delivery penalty. Mine, not yours, unless we write otherwise."

Lyssa's face sharpened.

"Why tell me?"

"Because if this becomes regular, I may offer you weekly committed lots. Those would have timing terms. First lot does not."

"What timing terms?"

"Not written yet."

"Then don't tell me maybe terms."

Marra paused.

"Fair."

Again.

Lyssa said, "First lot only. Six copper accepted return. Four days. Materials supplied. My seam errors I rework. Your cut/mark errors you own. Defects come back. No delivery penalty passed to me. One-day rear bench separately paid two bits today. Correct?"

Marra nodded.

"Correct."

Lyssa looked at me.

"What did you hear?"

Ah.

Not advice.

Witness.

I repeated it.

Exactly.

Marra corrected one phrase.

"My cut or written-mark error. Not every mark error. If Hall changes spec after cut, that is Hall change. I still handle it, but different category."

Lyssa said, "Fine. Add that."

Marra wrote note.

Then all three looked at it.

Good.

Not contract exactly.

Work card.

Clear enough.

Lyssa said, "Okay. First lot."

Marra held out hand.

Lyssa shook it.

I did not.

Not my deal.

That distinction felt better than expected.

I left for house.

On route I realized I had become errand boy.

Partner had lasted seventeen minutes.

Roles were flexible.

At home Cala was still at table.

Tavi gone outside with explicit permission, apparently.

"She took it?" Cala asked.

"First lot. Six copper if accepted. Four days. She's using rear bench today for two bits."

Cala nodded once.

Not shocked.

"Good."

"She wants shears, yellow needle roll, pressing cloth, food."

"She said yellow?"

"Yes."

"Good. Blue has coarse needles."

Useful.

I went upstairs.

Left drawer.

Two rolls.

Yellow.

Blue.

I took yellow.

Shears in leather sleeve.

Pressing cloth behind stove downstairs.

Cala wrapped bread, cheese, apple.

"For both?"

"She said food."

"Take enough for both."

"I wasn't invited to work."

"You will be there at midday."

This was household logistics, not prophecy.

She was right.

I carried everything back in shoulder bag.

Marra's rear workroom was hotter now.

Two pressing irons sat near small stove.

Lyssa had first apron pieces laid out.

Jessa occupied opposite bench with dark green sleeve guards.

I handed tools.

Lyssa checked yellow roll.

"You listened."

"Occasionally."

"Food?"

I held bag.

"Cala assumed I also eat."

"Smart woman."

Marra called from front, "Don't put food on work bench."

Lyssa called back, "I know."

Jessa said, "She says it because Fera did."

"I don't care why she says it."

"There was mustard."

Lyssa stopped.

"On cloth?"

"White cuff."

"Why was Fera eating mustard near white cuff?"

Jessa spread hands.

"Now you understand why you're less annoying."

Different register.

Longer gossip.

Good.

I could leave.

I probably should.

Lyssa threaded needle.

Then said, without looking up, "You can stay for lunch."

"Furniture?"

"Lunch furniture."

I stayed.

Not all afternoon.

That mattered too.

I watched first seam because she asked me to check whether cloth bundle at table edge blocked walkway for my crutches.

It did.

We moved it.

Then I sat by wall and read an old public broadsheet somebody had left on chair.

No investigation.

Mostly shipping notices and argument about canal toll.

Lyssa worked.

Her speech changed when hands busy.

Less dry.

More muttering.

"No, that's stupid. Why would you put notch there? Fine. Fine, I see why. Still ugly. Jessa, is this quarter turn or half?"

"Half on brown. Quarter on gray."

"Card says quarter."

"Old card."

Lyssa stopped.

"Marra."

Marra came.

They corrected card.

No secret test this time.

Good.

At lunch we sat on back step.

Lyssa ate apple first.

"You think I should've taken it?"

There.

Permission.

"Yes."

"Why?"

"First lot is bounded. Materials supplied. Six copper is meaningful. You paid two bits to learn pace before bringing rest home. You kept penalty off first lot. And you can still hate it after four days."

She chewed.

"Too many reasons."

"You asked."

"I know."

"Also Marra clearly wants you because you're good."

She looked away.

Not embarrassed exactly.

Something adjacent.

"She wants cheap finishing."

"Also true."

She looked back.

"See, that's why I ask you."

"Because I ruin compliment?"

"Because you don't make me choose between compliment and math."

That was better than compliment.

I ate bread.

"Six isn't shop rate," I said.

"No."

"But you aren't paying materials or shop overhead except today bench."

"No."

"How many hours?"

"Don't know yet. That's why today."

"Then today matters."

"Yes."

She leaned back against wall.

"If it takes me four full days, six is bad."

"Probably."

"If I can do it around other work in maybe two long days plus pieces, better."

"Yes."

"If second lot is ten and bigger, depends how much bigger."

"Yes."

She frowned.

"You're annoying when agreeing."

"I can disagree randomly."

"Don't."

We ate.

After lunch I left.

No reason to sit there proving support.

That would become surveillance with good intentions.

Lyssa did not ask me to stay.

I did not make her.

At door she said, "Can you tell Cala I might be late?"

"How late?"

"Before dinner. Probably."

"That is not late."

"For starting dinner work it is."

"I'll tell her."

"Thanks."

Then she went back to bench.

I went home.

Cala heard report.

"Good," she said.

"You say good a lot."

"When things are good."

"Disturbing consistency."

She handed me turnips.

"Cut."

Apparently my role changed again.

I cut turnips badly enough that Cala took knife after third.

"Too thick."

"They become thinner when chewed."

"Move."

I moved.

Tavi returned with mud on one knee and no explanation worth believing.

Normal afternoon.

Lyssa came home before dinner.

Tired.

Not miserable.

Important difference.

She dropped into chair.

"Four aprons. Four sleeve pairs."

"Accepted?" Cala asked.

"Marra checked first two of each. Clean. Rest stay until lot return."

"Pace?" I asked.

Lyssa rubbed fingers.

"Better after second. If home setup doesn't fight me, maybe two and half days total work. Spread over four."

"Six still good?"

She thought.

"Good enough to finish first lot."

Not same as yes forever.

Good.

She looked at me.

"You told Cala terms?"

"Only six, four days, supplied materials, two-bit bench today."

"Penalty?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Not household-relevant unless future lot."

She nodded.

Then paused.

"Thank you."

"For carrying cloth?"

"For not making it yours."

That landed harder than six copper.

I covered with stupidity.

"I considered stealing contract."

"You'd sew badly."

"I could learn."

"No."

"Hostile workplace."

She smiled.

Later upstairs I opened notebook.

My own ledger first.

CASH: 8 C

VALE: 23 C

Nothing changed.

Then I almost wrote Lyssa's six-copper job.

Stopped.

Her money.

Her work.

Not my ledger.

I wrote only:

LYSSA / MARRA FIRST LOT

ASK BEFORE ADVICE

IF ASKED TO WITNESS TERMS, REPEAT TERMS

DO NOT BECOME OWNER OF DECISION

Then I stared at last line.

Partner was dangerous word.

People used it to mean business.

Romance.

Work.

Crime.

Cards.

Two people carrying opposite ends of table.

Today it meant simpler thing.

She wanted me beside her while she decided something that belonged to her.

So I stood beside her.

Then I left when she didn't need me.

Partner.

For that.

Enough.

# CHAPTER 366

## THE DANCER

I was invited dancing because somebody hated me.

That was my first theory.

My second was worse.

"You want to go?" Lyssa asked.

She stood by table with hair half tied and charcoal coat open.

No cloth bundle.

No work card.

No emergency.

Suspicious.

"Where?"

"Lantern Court."

I waited.

"For?"

"Music."

"Why?"

She stared at me.

Cala, from stove, said, "Because people go listen to music."

"At home music cannot trample me."

Tavi looked up from floor.

"You can't dance."

There.

Household support.

"I can dance."

"With crutches?"

"I had legs before you were born."

"You have legs now."

"One and a half."

Cala said, "Greg."

"One and some paperwork."

Lyssa laughed.

Tavi looked at my left knee like maybe paperwork lived there.

"Can you?"

Good question.

Could I dance now?

First life, yes.

Not professionally.

Not well enough to impress anyone who mattered.

But enough taverns, festivals, weddings, victory feasts, terrible diplomatic dinners, and one memorable siege-ceasefire where three kingdoms pretended nobody had poisoned a duke.

This body?

Different problem.

"Probably badly," I said.

Lyssa said, "Good."

"That was fast."

"I don't want good dancing. I want to go out."

There.

No productivity.

No contract.

No object.

No money plan.

Just out.

My brain immediately searched for reasons not to.

Crowd.

Uneven paving.

Standing too long.

Crutches around feet.

Unknown dance style.

Possibility of being nineteen in public.

Last one severe.

Lyssa watched all of that happen on my face.

"You can say no."

That made saying no harder.

Not manipulation.

Freedom.

Annoying.

"When?"

"After dinner."

"How long?"

"Greg."

"Reasonable question."

"Until we want to leave."

Terrible answer.

Excellent answer.

"Fine."

Tavi said, "I want to see."

"No," Cala said.

"Why?"

"Because you're staying here."

"Why?"

"Because I said so."

Tavi looked at me.

I looked away.

Not my jurisdiction.

After dinner Lyssa changed nothing except hair.

That surprised me.

I expected some ritual.

Different dress.

Different shoes.

Something.

She wore charcoal coat over ordinary clothes, hair tied back with dark ribbon, boots she already owned.

I wore what I had.

Brown shoulder bag stayed home.

Oval stayed home with it.

That felt strange.

I almost went back for bag.

Then didn't.

No artifact.

No notebook.

No wrapped charcoal.

Just me.

Disgusting.

Lantern Court sat north of market district where three streets widened around old fountain basin that no longer held water.

Tonight it held musicians.

Not literally in basin.

Mostly.

A low platform had been laid across one half.

Five players.

Two hand drums.

Long-necked string instrument.

Short brass horn.

And something like wooden ribs hung from frame, struck with padded sticks.

Each rib glowed faintly when hit.

Not bright.

Amber pulse along grain.

Sound carried farther than it should have.

I noticed.

Then did not investigate.

Growth remained exhausting.

Lanterns hung on cords across court.

Actual lanterns.

No flying fish.

People stood around edges with cups, bread, skewers, folded paper trays.

Center paving mostly clear.

Maybe forty people.

Not formal.

Not performance exactly.

Music for being there.

Lyssa stopped at edge.

"Still want to leave?"

"I never said I wanted to leave."

"Your shoulders did."

I loosened them.

"Traitors."

She smiled.

First song already moving.

Not one I knew.

Pattern simple enough.

Four-beat phrase with third dragged slightly late.

People in center danced mostly in pairs or loose groups.

Steps lateral.

Turn.

Back.

Cross.

Some hand contact.

Some not.

My first-life body supplied six wrong dances immediately.

None useful.

Lyssa said, "Drink?"

"What?"

"You look like you're preparing military campaign."

"Terrain matters."

"It's a courtyard."

"Historically dangerous terrain."

She bought herself something warm from stall.

I did not ask price.

She did not offer me one.

Good.

I stood near low wall where I could lean hip if needed.

We listened through first song.

Then second.

Lyssa moved with music without dancing.

Small shift in shoulders.

Heel tapping.

I watched center.

Not because afraid.

Because information gathering was noble and not cowardice.

An older man danced with woman using cane.

Not same as crutches.

Useful anyway.

She kept cane mostly planted during turns, pivoted around right foot, let partner move more.

Another pair barely moved feet at all.

Just weight shift and hands.

Nobody cared.

That was problem.

If nobody cared, I lost excuse.

Lyssa finished drink.

"Come on."

"No."

She laughed.

"You said yes."

"To going out."

"Correct."

She did not pull me.

She walked into edge of dance space alone.

Then turned around.

Not challenging.

Just there.

I hated this.

I went.

First problem was crutch tips.

Not slipping.

Paving dry.

But people stepped backward without looking.

I kept to outer ring.

Lyssa stood facing me.

"What do I do?"

"You said you could dance."

"I lied under pressure."

"There was no pressure."

"Exactly. More dangerous."

She showed step.

Left.

Together.

Right.

Together.

Small turn on fourth phrase.

I looked down.

"Don't."

"I need feet."

"You know where yours is."

"Optimistic."

"Greg."

I looked up.

We tried.

Left for her meant right for me.

Easy with right foot.

Together meant bring crutches, then body.

Other direction meant left knee and crutch geometry.

No foot to place.

Could shift pelvis over prosthetic absence only in ways this body did not permit because there was no prosthesis.

Permanent BKA knee preserved.

No fake limb.

So standard step broke.

I stopped.

Lyssa stopped.

No pity.

Good.

"Different," she said.

"Profound."

"Can you turn?"

"Yes."

"Show me."

I planted right crutch slightly forward, left crutch wider, right foot pivot.

Quarter turn.

Reset.

Another.

Not fast.

Stable.

Lyssa watched.

"Okay."

She changed her own step.

Not teacher anymore.

Testing shared geometry.

She moved around me on dragged third beat while I shifted weight and pivoted smaller.

Then returned.

"Again."

Again.

This worked.

Barely dance.

Definitely movement.

Third try, I caught rhythm.

Right foot step.

Crutches together.

Weight shift.

Small pivot.

Lyssa crossed past left side instead of expecting me to travel there.

Return.

My shoulders stopped trying to solve.

Music did something irritating.

Body followed.

Not old body.

This one.

That distinction arrived without permission.

I missed beat.

Lyssa said, "There."

"What?"

"You were doing it."

"Then you spoke."

"Sorry."

"Sabotage."

She laughed and nearly stepped on right crutch.

I moved it.

"Danger."

"Your stick attacked me."

"Crutch."

"Aggressive crutch."

We continued.

One song.

Then I stopped.

Not because pain.

Hands tired faster than expected.

Forearms warm.

Right calf doing more than fair share.

"Wall," I said.

Lyssa nodded.

No `are you okay` parade.

We went back to edge.

I leaned.

She stood beside me breathing slightly harder.

"That was dancing," she said.

"Legally?"

"Barely."

"I'll appeal."

A woman near us laughed.

Not at crutches.

At sentence.

She was maybe thirty, broad face, red scarf, holding paper tray of fried roots.

"He'll lose," she told Lyssa.

"I know."

I said, "Corrupt court."

Woman ate root.

"All dance courts corrupt."

Then she walked away.

No introduction.

Perfect stranger interaction.

Third song faster.

We watched.

A group of four took center, spinning in linked pattern.

No chance.

Not yet.

Maybe never.

Fine.

Lyssa said, "You don't have to do every one."

"I know."

"You looked offended."

"By their knees."

"Their knees?"

"Showing off."

She laughed again.

Fourth song slower.

Different pattern.

People paired closer.

Less traveling.

Lyssa looked at me.

I said, "Maybe."

"That's almost enthusiasm."

"Don't ruin it."

We went back.

This time I chose movement first.

No attempt to copy standard lateral step.

Right foot marked beat.

Crutches carried rhythm as much as body.

Tap.

Plant.

Shift.

Pivot.

Lyssa adapted.

Her hands stayed mostly free until she held my forearm once during turn.

Not support.

Contact.

Important difference.

I felt it anyway.

Music changed phrase.

She moved closer.

I moved crutch out before she arrived.

Competence.

Tiny.

Useful.

Then somebody behind me backed into left crutch.

Hard enough to knock tip sideways.

My body reacted before thought.

Right crutch planted.

Right foot loaded.

Left knee lifted clear.

I caught balance.

The man spun around.

"Shit. Sorry. Sorry, I didn't see."

He was young, maybe my age, flushed from dancing.

Hands up.

"I'm good."

"You sure?"

"Yes."

He looked at crutch.

"I kicked it."

"It survived."

"I can get someone."

"For crutch feelings?"

He blinked.

Lyssa said, "He's fine."

"Right. Sorry."

He backed away more carefully.

No fight.

No humiliation.

No public lesson.

Just collision.

My heart had jumped harder than incident deserved.

Body remembered falling better than pride admitted.

Lyssa saw that.

Didn't say it.

She asked, "Wall?"

I considered.

Then shook head.

"Finish song."

Her expression changed.

Small.

Not praise.

Okay.

We finished.

More careful.

Not frozen.

That mattered.

At end I went wall voluntarily.

Hands definitely tired now.

I rubbed palms.

No blister.

No skin issue.

Lyssa leaned beside me.

"Want to leave?"

I looked around.

Lanterns.

Amber ribs pulsing with each struck note.

People talking over music.

Fried-root smell.

A child asleep across someone's shoulders near fountain edge.

Two women arguing whether horn player had skipped verse.

The man who hit my crutch now dancing six feet farther away from everyone.

I smiled.

"Not yet."

Lyssa said nothing.

Good.

We stayed two more songs without dancing.

Then one where she danced with a woman she apparently knew from cloth district.

I had never met her.

Lyssa introduced us only after.

"Greg, Pera. Pera, Greg."

Pera had curls tied in yellow cloth and spoke as if sentences needed extra rooms.

"Oh, you're Greg. I thought you were taller, which is stupid because Lyssa never said tall, she said you use crutches and somehow my brain decided that meant tall, maybe because crutches are long, which makes no sense because then I'd think everyone with broom was tall."

I liked her immediately.

"I am devastated."

"See, that. She said you do that."

Lyssa said, "I did not say that."

"You did. Not those words. You made face."

"What face?"

Pera made an expression that looked like someone smelling competent cheese.

Lyssa stared.

I said, "Accurate."

"Don't encourage her."

Pera pointed at my crutches.

"You danced?"

"Allegedly."

"Good. My uncle dances with one leg and a cane but he cheats because he was already terrible before losing it, so nobody can tell what changed."

Lyssa made choking sound.

I laughed.

Actual laugh.

Pera continued, "He says same thing. Anyway I need water. Bye."

She left.

I looked at Lyssa.

"You have been hiding her."

"For safety."

"Mine or hers?"

"Everyone's."

Good.

Dialogue variance alive.

Last dance we did was not really a dance.

Musicians slowed into closing piece.

People mostly swayed.

Lyssa stood beside me rather than opposite.

I kept right crutch planted, left angled, right foot tapping.

She leaned shoulder lightly into mine.

No turn.

No step pattern.

Just beat.

I could do beat.

First life supplied memories anyway.

A hall with polished floor.

A woman whose name I had forgotten before she died.

A winter festival in capital that no longer existed in same form.

Boots.

Two feet.

No crutches.

Older hands.

Older scars.

Then Lantern Court came back.

Nineteen.

One right foot.

Left knee.

Two crutches.

Lyssa's shoulder.

Amber ribs.

Not replacement.

Nothing replaced anything.

That was childish arithmetic.

This was another thing.

Song ended.

We walked home slowly.

Not because I couldn't faster.

Because neither of us did.

Halfway home Lyssa said, "You liked it."

"Parts."

"You liked it."

"I liked Pera."

"Dangerous."

"I liked fried-root smell."

"You didn't buy any."

"I can appreciate without acquisition."

"Write that down."

"Never."

She bumped my shoulder lightly.

"You danced."

I considered denying.

Didn't.

"Yeah."

At house Cala was awake.

Tavi was not.

"Good?" she asked.

Lyssa said, "He danced."

Cala looked at me.

"Did he?"

"Barely."

"Traitor."

Cala smiled.

"Good."

Of course.

Upstairs I sat on bed and checked hands.

Red pressure marks.

Normal.

Right calf tired.

Shoulders tired.

No injury.

No fall.

No magic.

No money earned.

No debt reduced.

No artifact learned.

No thread resolved.

I had gone somewhere because Lyssa wanted to go.

Then stayed because I wanted to stay.

I almost opened notebook.

Didn't.

Nothing needed recording.

That felt wrong enough to be useful.

I lay back.

Dancer.

Bad one.

Still counted.

# CHAPTER 367

## THE INSTRUCTOR

Jori wanted me to teach three stagehands how not to die.

This was flattering until I met stagehands.

"They're not idiots," she said.

We stood inside theatre side door while rain tapped roof hard enough to make conversation private.

"That is a worrying opening."

"We had two near collisions last week."

"What am I teaching?"

"Moving under bad light."

That was more specific.

Jori pointed toward rehearsal floor.

Theatre had changed since last time.

Not structurally.

People.

A half-painted wall flat leaned near rear rail. Two rope coils sat on marked boards. Someone had chalked three pale lanes across floor from wings to center and then erased half of one badly.

Nessa stood on ladder adjusting hood over practice lamp.

Not Three Reeds.

Normal theatre equipment.

Good.

"We had two near collisions last week during blackout shift," Jori said. "No injuries. One dropped prop. One person walked into frame because they were watching glow mark instead of route."

"Why me?"

"Because you move with occupied hands and limited step options every day."

I looked at crutches.

"My hands are not occupied. They are infrastructure."

"Fine. Because your infrastructure makes you think about route before moving."

Better.

"And because you noticed rear spill during Three Reeds test before Nessa did."

From ladder Nessa said, "I heard that."

Jori raised voice.

"Good."

Nessa said something rude under breath.

Dialogue alive.

"I am not theatre safety instructor," I said.

"I know."

"I am not rigging specialist."

"I know."

"I am not blackout movement specialist."

Jori looked at me.

"Do you know how to walk through dark room without hitting things?"

"Usually."

"Can you explain why?"

Maybe.

"How much?"

Jori smiled slightly.

There he is.

"Two copper. Two hours. Today. You teach route discipline under low light. I own theatre rules. Nessa owns lamp. You do not certify anyone."

Clear.

"Three people?"

"Three."

"Who?"

"Mett, Olo, Brenna."

Brenna I knew by sight maybe.

Mett maybe not.

Olo definitely not.

No extortion Bren.

Different name.

Thank gods.

"What does success mean?"

Jori's expression changed.

Not annoyed.

Interested.

"They can cross marked route under reduced light, stop on lost orientation instead of guessing, communicate obstruction, and restart from known point."

"Carrying?"

"Empty first. Then one light prop each."

"No speed test."

"Agreed."

"No surprise obstacles."

"Agreed."

"No pretending actual blackout if enough light remains to cheat."

Nessa climbed down.

"That one is mine."

"Can you make it dark enough?"

She looked offended.

"Yes."

"Safely?"

More offended.

"Jori."

Jori said, "He asks everyone like that."

"I dislike him."

"You liked him last time."

"Conditions changed."

I said, "Healthy relationship."

Nessa pointed at me.

"Two hours. Then leave."

Good.

I accepted.

Two copper mattered.

Not desperate money.

Still money.

Cash would go eight to ten if paid.

Vale stayed twenty-three.

Different stair.

The three stagehands arrived together.

Mett was broad, maybe twenty-five, hair shaved at sides, talked before fully entering room.

"Jori said this isn't a test, which means it is probably a test in the way people say something isn't a test right before they remember it later when deciding whether you get shifts."

Jori said, "It isn't a test."

Mett nodded.

"Exactly."

Brenna was older than me by maybe ten years, narrow face, sleeves rolled unevenly, expression skeptical.

Olo was younger, maybe seventeen, tall and quiet enough that silence itself seemed intentional.

Three different problems already.

I stood at chalk line.

"I'm Greg."

Mett said, "We know."

Brenna said, "He knows. I know you have crutches. That's different."

Olo said nothing.

Excellent.

"Jori hired me for two hours to teach route discipline under low light. I am not grading you. I am not certifying you. If Jori secretly grades you afterward, haunt her."

Jori, behind me, said, "Useful opening."

"I establish trust."

"Poorly."

Mett raised hand.

"Can we haunt while employed?"

"Advanced course."

Brenna almost smiled.

Good.

I pointed to chalk route.

"Show me how you cross now. Full light."

Mett went first.

Fast.

Too fast.

He looked at destination, not floor.

Fine in full light.
