Brenna slower, checked edges, stepped around rope coil even though outside route.

Olo followed chalk exactly.

Exactly enough to be problem.

At erased section he hesitated, then guessed where line continued.

There.

Three habits.

"Again," I said.

Mett groaned.

"Already?"

"We have achieved repetition."

"I hate training."

"Useful information."

Second crossing I stopped each at midpoint.

"Where is nearest known boundary?"

Mett looked around.

"Wing post."

Brenna said, "Rear rail."

Olo pointed at chalk under foot.

"That isn't boundary," I said.

He looked at me.

"It's route."

"What if mark disappears?"

He shrugged.

First words came quiet.

"Then I know roughly."

"Roughly is how frame gets forehead."

Brenna said, "That happened to Mett."

Mett touched forehead.

"Once."

"Last week."

"Still once."

Good.

I walked route myself.

Not to demonstrate speed.

Opposite.

"I cannot make lateral correction quickly to left."

I tapped left crutch.

"So before moving I want three things. Start point. Next boundary. Stop condition."

Mett said, "That's very dramatic for twelve steps."

"Twelve steps can still end in wall."

"Fair."

"Start point is where you know you are. Next boundary is something physical or fixed enough to orient from. Stop condition is what makes you stop rather than inventing route."

Brenna crossed arms.

"We have glow marks."

"Good. What happens when prop blocks one?"

"Use next."

"What happens when next is behind you?"

She frowned.

"Then stop."

"Yes."

"That's it?"

"Mostly."

She looked disappointed.

People wanted expertise to be secret.

Usually it was boring things done before panic.

Nessa reduced lamps.

Room dimmed.

Not dark.

Then dimmer.

Amber route marks near floor became visible.

Different from chalk.

Small painted tabs with weak stored glow.

I knew general family.

No need lecture.

"First pass," I said. "No props. No speed. Say boundary before moving."

Mett stared.

"Out loud?"

"Yes."

"This is humiliating."

"Good. Humiliation improves memory."

Jori said, "Not official theatre doctrine."

"Coward."

Mett went.

"Wing post."

Step.

"Low mark."

Step.

"Center seam."

He reached erased-chalk section, but low-light route used glow tab at wall instead.

"Rear lamp bracket."

Better.

Brenna did same with fewer words.

Olo moved too soon after naming mark, before eyes adjusted from brighter wing.

I stopped him.

"Why?"

He looked annoyed.

Good.

"I saw it."

"You saw one thing."

"That's enough."

"No."

Silence.

I knew this kind.

Not stupid.

Hated being corrected in public.

Maybe especially by nineteen-year-old with crutches.

Fair enough.

"Come here," I said.

He did.

I pointed toward route.

"Look at center mark."

He did.

"Now tell me what's left of it."

"Frame."

"Right?"

"Rope."

"Behind?"

He glanced.

"Wing post."

"Now move."

He crossed cleanly.

"You weren't waiting for mark," I said. "You were waiting for room."

He nodded once.

That was enough.

Second pass Nessa reduced light again.

Mett said, "Can she stop doing that?"

Nessa said, "No."

"I respect consistency."

Now room was dark enough that faces became suggestions.

Glow marks stronger by contrast.

Jori stayed near wall.

I heard her more than saw.

"Carry props," she said.

Three objects waited.

Wooden bowl.

Rolled cloth.

Light frame corner.

Mett took bowl.

Brenna cloth.

Olo frame.

"No," I said.

Olo stopped.

"Why?"

"Frame changes width. You haven't practiced width."

Jori said, "Good catch."

I hated praise during instruction.

"Brenna, frame. Olo, cloth."

Brenna looked at frame.

"Why me?"

"You already route around things you don't need to. I want see if you overprotect width."

"That's rude."

"Educational."

She took it.

Mett moved first.

Fine until bowl blocked low glow mark when held naturally.

He leaned sideways to find it.

"Stop," I said.

He froze.

Good.

"What do you know?"

"Wing post behind."

"Next boundary?"

"Center seam."

"Can you find without mark?"

He shifted foot carefully.

"Floor seam."

"Use it."

He did.

No drama.

Brenna with frame did exactly what predicted.

She kept staring at frame corner.

Not route.

"You're escorting object," I said.

"I don't want hit anything."

"Then know where you are. Object comes with you."

"Easy for you to say."

"I bring two long objects everywhere."

Pause.

Mett laughed first.

Brenna looked at crutches.

"Fine. Annoyingly fair."

She reset.

Second try better.

Olo with rolled cloth moved cleanly until Nessa's hood shifted slightly.

Light angle changed.

One glow tab became weaker.

He stopped.

Immediately.

No guess.

Good.

"What happened?" I asked.

"Mark changed."

"What do you do?"

"Stop. Find room."

"Do it."

He did.

There.

Teaching was mostly waiting for someone to make correct mistake.

Third round I added communication.

Not surprise obstacles.

Known obstruction.

A low empty crate placed partly across route.

Everyone saw it under full light first.

Then Nessa dimmed room.

"You're not testing memory of crate," I said. "You're practicing what happens when route isn't clear. First person finds it. What do you say?"

Mett said, "Crate."

"Where?"

"In way."

"Where?"

"Middle."

"Middle of what?"

He sighed deeply.

"Route between wing post and center seam, low obstruction, right half blocked."

"Good."

"That was many words."

"Fewer than ambulance."

Jori said, "We don't call it that."

"Whatever Carrow calls carrying idiot to healer."

Brenna said, "Stretcher team."

"Fewer than stretcher team."

Mett nodded.

"Less punchy."

They practiced.

Brenna's report was best.

Olo's too short.

"Low block, right."

"Which segment?"

"Wing to center."

"Good."

Then Mett tried to be helpful and moved crate in dark.

I heard scrape.

"Stop."

He froze.

"What?"

"Who told you move it?"

"Nobody."

"Then don't change map everyone else is using."

That landed.

Jori said, "Again."

Mett said, "I was fixing it."

Jori answered, "You were creating new condition without telling anyone."

Different voice from mine.

Authority.

Good.

We reset.

No punishment.

Again.

After an hour my throat hurt more than hands.

Unexpected occupational hazard.

We took break.

Nessa raised lamps halfway.

Mett sat on floor.

Brenna leaned against frame.

Olo remained standing.

I drank water Jori gave me.

Mett said, "So how do you do stairs?"

Not malicious.

Curious.

"Carefully."

"No, I mean with both crutches."

"Depends stairs. Rail changes everything."

"No rail?"

"Up or down?"

"Both."

"You want second course."

He grinned.

Brenna said, "Don't ask him. We'll be here all day."

"I thought he liked talking."

"He likes asking questions. Different disease."

I pointed at her.

"You learned quickly."

Olo said, "Why did Jori pick you?"

Direct.

I looked at him.

"She said because I think about route before moving."

"Because leg?"

"Partly."

He nodded.

No discomfort.

"Makes sense."

Then drank water.

That was entire conversation.

I liked him more.

Second hour we made routes less verbal.

Full spoken boundary every move was training scaffold, not actual backstage behavior.

Jori wanted short calls.

We settled on three useful ones already close to theatre language:

HOLD.

BLOCK.

CLEAR.

Jori owned exact meanings.

HOLD meant stop movement, keep position.

BLOCK meant route segment obstructed, followed by location.

CLEAR meant stated segment checked and open, not whole room safe forever.

I did not invent theatre vocabulary.

Important.

We practiced pairs.

One moved.

One watched route from fixed point.

Then swapped.

I became instructor instead of participant.

Strange.

First life had taught thousands of things.

Sword grip.

Mana circulation.

Field dressing.

How to sleep in wet armor.

How to identify wyvern spoor before breakfast.

How to tell young adventurer that bravery was not substitute for map.

I had trained recruits who later became captains.

Some died anyway.

Most teaching did not make people immortal.

It just removed stupid deaths from pile.

That was enough.

Mett rushed once.

I stopped him.

Brenna overcalled obstruction once.

Jori corrected.

Olo lost mark and stopped correctly twice.

Second time he found wing post by sound of Nessa adjusting lamp hood.

"No," I said.

He frowned.

"Why?"

"Nessa moves. Post doesn't."

He considered.

"Right."

"Use fixed boundary."

He did.

Good.

Near end Jori said, "One clean sequence. Then done."

Nessa lowered light.

Mett carried bowl.

Brenna carried frame.

Olo cloth.

Known crate obstruction remained.

No surprises.

Mett reached first segment.

Stopped when bowl hid mark.

Found floor seam.

Moved.

"BLOCK. Wing-center, right half."

Brenna answered from behind.

"HOLD."

Mett held.

Brenna repositioned frame before entering segment, keeping width inside left half.

"CLEAR left."

She crossed.

Olo waited.

Not because told.

Because route occupied.

Then moved after Brenna cleared.

His weak mark disappeared under changed angle again.

He stopped.

Looked around room, not mark.

Found rear rail.

Continued.

No collision.

No dropped prop.

No speed.

Boring.

Beautiful.

Nessa raised lights.

Mett said, "That's it?"

Jori said, "That's it."

"I expected something harder at end."

"Why?"

"Training always has hard thing at end."

I said, "Real work is hard thing. Training should make it less stupid."

Brenna nodded.

"I like that."

"Don't. I may charge."

Olo said, "You already are."

I stared at him.

Mett laughed loud enough to echo.

Olo almost smiled.

Dangerous.

Jori dismissed them.

Mett left talking about food.

Brenna reminded him he owed her something from yesterday.

Olo carried practice cloth back without being asked.

Jori waited until door shut.

"Good."

"Cala says that."

"She sounds wise."

"Terrifying."

Jori handed me two copper.

Actual coins.

I counted.

Two.

No bits.

Blessed simplicity.

Cash ten.

"If we do this again," Jori said, "I want you and Nessa to build one route drill for prop changes."

I looked at her.

"Does work exist?"

She smiled.

"Not yet."

"Then ask when it exists."

"I knew you'd say that."

"Why waste both our time?"

"Because sometimes people enjoy future planning."

"Unhealthy."

Nessa called from lamp.

"I still dislike him."

Jori said, "Noted."

I left theatre with ten copper and sore throat.

Rain had stopped.

Street smelled washed.

I took long route home for no reason except Lantern Court had apparently damaged me.

At one corner two delivery boys were trying to carry a narrow table through door sideways.

One said, "Lift your end."

Other said, "I am lifting."

They hit frame.

I kept walking.

Not instructor everywhere.

Important boundary.

At home Lyssa had brown carry-wrap pieces spread over table.

First lot still in progress.

Cala had moved dinner preparation to side counter.

Tavi sat under table doing something with string that looked illegal but probably wasn't.

Lyssa looked up.

"How was teaching?"

"No one died."

"High standard."

"They improved."

"Higher standard."

I put two copper with my money upstairs before dinner.

Ten.

Vale twenty-three.

For first time in a while, ten copper did not feel like fortune.

It felt like room.

Not enough for everything.

Enough that next interesting object could be a decision instead of joke.

Dangerous thought.

I wrote:

CASH: 10 C

VALE: 23 C

THEATRE ROUTE SESSION: +2 C

Then below:

START POINT

NEXT FIXED BOUNDARY

STOP CONDITION

DO NOT MOVE OBSTRUCTION WITHOUT CALL

I almost wrote more.

Didn't need to.

Teaching had reminded me of something old.

Not technique.

Role.

Instructor.

First life, I had worn it so long I stopped noticing.

People came because I knew more.

Sometimes because rank required them.

Sometimes because they were afraid.

Sometimes because they wanted to survive.

Today three stagehands had come because Jori paid me two copper and told them to.

Less majestic.

Probably healthier.

I closed notebook.

Instructor.

Still knew how.

# CHAPTER 368

## THE BIDDER

I learned about the auction because Sella said, "You like bad objects."

This was slander.

Accurate slander remained slander.

We met on South Cut by accident.

Actual accident.

No bridge rising.

No ropes snapping.

No one needed evacuation.

Sella stood beside a tea cart with one hand around clay cup and the other holding folded notice.

She saw me.

Looked at notice.

Looked back at me.

"You like bad objects."

"I like misunderstood objects."

"Same disease with better coat."

"What's notice?"

She handed it over.

CITY RECOVERY SALE

NORTH WEIGH YARD

SECOND BELL TOMORROW

UNCLAIMED / RETIRED / DAMAGED SERVICE GOODS

INSPECTION FROM FIRST BELL

NO POWERED TESTING ON SALE FLOOR

SOLD AS MARKED

BUYER REMOVES SAME DAY

Below that were lot families.

Hand tools.

Office fixtures.

Lamp housings.

Counterweights.

Route markers.

Kitchen service goods.

Two lines marked SPECIAL HANDLING.

No individual lots.

My pulse did something embarrassing.

Sella drank tea.

"There."

"What?"

"Face."

"I have face."

"Object face."

Apparently everyone had taxonomy for me now.

"Why aren't you going?"

"Because I work for living."

"Cruel."

"Also most recovery sale is junk."

"Most."

"Greg."

I folded notice.

"What does sold as marked mean here?"

"Whatever condition card says is what yard represents. They don't promise hidden function, remaining service life, compatibility, or repair cost. If card says cracked housing, you bought cracked housing. If card says retired, you bought retired."

"Inspection handling?"

"Usually yes unless red cord. No activation. No disassembly."

"Deposit?"

"No bidder deposit for public lots under silver threshold. Pay immediately if you win."

"Cash only?"

"Copper, silver, registered yard credit."

I had copper.

Ten.

Ten suddenly became less like room and more like loaded weapon.

Sella took notice back, then stopped.

"You want it?"

"Yes."

She gave it to me.

"Don't buy counterweight because it looks lonely."

"I have standards."

"You bought retired rest stone."

"It had history."

"Disease."

She walked away.

I stood there reading notice again.

No individual lots.

No guarantee anything interesting existed.

Could go and buy nothing.

Healthy people did that.

I knew several.

Probably.

At home Cala read notice once.

"Same-day removal."

Of course she found storage line first.

"Yes."

"Chest is not bigger because you have ten copper."

"I know."

Lyssa was stitching sleeve guard at table.

She didn't look up.

"Do you?"

"Hostile household."

"Storage household."

Tavi asked, "Can I come?"

Three adults said no.

He looked betrayed by democracy.

I set notice beside plate.

"I'm only inspecting."

Lyssa looked up now.

"That's worse sentence than 'I'm only looking.'"

"Inspection is more disciplined."

"You bought lantern after looking."

"Shade unit."

"See?"

Cala said, "If you buy something large, it doesn't come upstairs until we know where it goes."

Fair.

"Agreed."

"If it leaks?"

"I don't buy leaking."

"If seller says it doesn't leak?"

"I still inspect for leaking."

"If it starts leaking later?"

"Why is everything leaking?"

"Because you buy old magic."

Also fair.

I slept badly.

Not from worry.

Worse.

Anticipation.

First bell next morning I was at North Weigh Yard.

Healthy.

Normal.

North Weigh sat behind freight scales where stone lanes widened around roofed sheds.

Recovery sale occupied Shed Four.

A board at entrance repeated rules.

A clerk gave each bidder flat wooden paddle with painted number after name entry.

Mine was thirty-two.

No fee.

No bidder deposit.

"Payment due at lot close," clerk said. "Removal by sunset. Red-cord goods handled only by yard staff. Questions to floor clerks. Condition cards control."

"Can I withdraw bid before close?"

She looked at me like I had asked whether gravity negotiable.

"No."

Good rule to know.

Inside smelled like dust, iron, wet rope, old wood, lamp oil, and a little ozone.

There were maybe sixty lots.

Most were boring.

Beautifully boring.

Bent hooks.

Cracked wash basins.

Three bundles of mismatched shelf brackets.

A brass office bell with no striker.

Two hand lamps missing shutters.

A box of route tabs whose glow had faded to weak green.

A kitchen warming plate with one corner warped.

I walked everything once before touching anything.

First-life habit.

Auction rooms punished love at first sight.

Especially when love had condition card.

Lot twelve interested me.

RETIRED WALL INDEXER

MANUAL ROTATION FREE

MARKING CORE REMOVED

FRAME TRUE AT INTAKE

MOUNT PLATE INCLUDED

Six copper opening estimate written smaller below.

Without marking core it was furniture with ambition.

Passed.

Lot seventeen was red-cord.

A squat iron cylinder in wood cradle.

SERVICE PRESSURE RESERVOIR

RETIRED AFTER SEAL DRIFT

RESIDUAL CHARGE CLEARED AT INTAKE

DO NOT OPEN ON SALE FLOOR

Opening estimate four copper.

Absolutely not.

I loved it.

Absolutely not.

Growth.

Lot twenty-three was a set of three counterweight shoes.

Sella's voice entered brain.

Don't buy counterweight because it looks lonely.

I moved on out of spite.

Then lot thirty-one stopped me.

Small.

Good start.

Wooden case about length of forearm, shallow, with brass corners and two leather loops on back.

Inside, under lifted lid, sat six narrow ceramic slats arranged like ribs.

Not instrument.

Each slat had a blackened metal tongue at one end and faded number scratched near other.

Condition card:

LOT 31

RETIRED DRAFT-FINDER ARRAY

FORMER USE: WEST ARCHIVE STACKS

SIX OF EIGHT VANES PRESENT

TWO MISSING

CASE HINGE REPAIRED

PASSIVE RESPONSE OBSERVED AT INTAKE

CALIBRATION UNKNOWN

MOUNT STRAPS HARDENED

NO POWERED TESTING

No price estimate.

Interesting.

Draft finder.

Passive response.

Archive stacks.

Missing two of eight.

Not magic treasure.

Probably old environmental tool.

I looked for floor clerk.

Woman in orange sash approached.

"Question?"

"What does passive response observed mean on this card?"

"Intake clerk saw vane movement in airflow without activation source. That's all."

"Mana component?"

"Unknown. Former registry lists draft-finder array. Yard did not open tongues or test calibration."

"Why retired?"

She checked secondary tag hanging under card.

"Archive replacement cycle. Unit marked incomplete at removal. No fault code transferred."

"Can I lift case?"

"Yes. Don't remove vanes."

I lifted.

Maybe four pounds.

Fine.

Back loops could hang from wall or strap to frame.

I tilted slightly.

Ceramic slats barely moved.

No obvious loose rattle.

I set it down.

"Any compatible replacement vanes in sale?"

"Not listed as compatible."

Good answer.

"Thank you."

She moved on.

I stared.

What did I need draft finder for?

Nothing.

House had windows.

Theatre had smoke strips and professionals.

Travel maybe.

Bad answer.

Artifact desire liked to invent future selves.

I walked away.

Lot thirty-eight was better in obvious way.

A compact folding brazier with intact heat plate.

Condition card said serviceable manually, heat rune removed, ordinary charcoal use only.

Opening estimate two copper.

Useful.

Boring.

Could actually use.

We already had stove.

Passed.

Lot forty-four made me stop longer.

PAIR OF ROUTE ECHO PINS

RETIRED MUNICIPAL SURVEY

ONE RETURNS CLEAN TONE AT INTAKE

ONE RETURNS INTERMITTENT TONE

RANGE / PAIRING / CALIBRATION UNKNOWN

NO FIELD TEST

Two iron pegs with blue glass heads.

Potentially useful.

Potentially broken.

Potentially exactly kind of object that consumed three weeks and then taught me I owned two pegs.

I asked price expectation.

Floor clerk said no estimate.

I walked away.

That hurt.

Good.

At second bell auction started.

Not theatrical.

A yard caller stood on crate with clerk beside him.

Lots moved fast.

One through ten mostly sold for bits and low copper.

I did not bid.

Lot twelve wall indexer opened at four copper despite six estimate.

Two office furnishers fought to seven.

Visible salvage value.

Reasonable.

Lot seventeen pressure reservoir opened three.

A repair shop bidder took it at five.

Also reasonable.

They could service seals.

I could stare at seals.

Lot twenty-three counterweight shoes sold two copper to freight man.

I felt nothing.

Mostly.

Lot thirty-one arrived.

"Retired draft-finder array," caller said. "Six of eight vanes. Passive response observed. Calibration unknown. West Archive former service. Opening one copper."

One copper.

My paddle moved before brain approved.

"One, thirty-two."

Another paddle lifted.

"Two, fourteen."

Bidder fourteen was older woman with ink-stained fingers.

Maybe archivist.

Maybe collector.

Maybe murderer.

Irrelevant.

Two copper.

At two, object was still interesting.

At three?

I had ten.

Three left seven.

Vale twenty-three unchanged.

Could pay debt instead.

Could keep reserve.

Could buy actual food/tools later.

Draft finder did not solve current problem.

Paddle fourteen stayed up.

Caller looked around.

"Two copper. Any three?"

I raised.

"Three, thirty-two."

Woman fourteen looked at me.

Not hostile.

Assessing.

Raised.

"Four, fourteen."

Four.

This was where auctions became confession.

What did I believe object worth to me?

Not hidden market value.

Not theoretical magic.

To me.

Four copper was forty percent of liquid cash.

For incomplete unknown-calibration passive environmental instrument.

No.

I lowered paddle.

Caller waited.

"Four copper. Once. Twice. Sold fourteen."

Pain.

Clean pain.

Good pain.

Woman fourteen won draft finder.

I still had ten copper.

This was success.

I hated success.

Lot thirty-eight brazier sold three.

I did not bid.

Lot forty-four.

Echo pins.

Caller read card.

"Opening one copper."

No paddles.

Interesting.

"One copper?"

Still none.

My brain started inventing.

One clean tone.

One intermittent.

Municipal survey.

Could maybe establish direction or paired route response.

Could maybe be useless without missing receiver.

Card said pair.

Former pair, maybe.

Unknown pairing/calibration.

One copper was cheap because obvious condition bad and use uncertain.

This was exactly where information gap could exist.

Also exactly where garbage lived.

I raised paddle.

"One, thirty-two."

Caller looked.

"Two?"

A man near rear lifted paddle.

"Two, forty-one."

Of course.

He wore surveyer's gray vest.

Actual likely user.

Bad sign for bargain.

Good sign for object relevance.

I looked at pins.

Two copper.

Could justify.

Maybe.

I raised three.

"Three, thirty-two."

Survey man raised four immediately.

No hesitation.

There.

He knew something or had compatible equipment.

Either way market signal.

Four.

Would I go five?

Half cash.

For one intermittent retired pin pair with unknown range/calibration.

No.

Paddle down.

"Four, forty-one. Once. Twice. Sold."

Second loss.

Strangely easier.

Because bidder made sense.

Market wasn't asleep waiting for me.

Good.

Auction continued.

I almost left.

Then lot fifty-two appeared.

Not artifact.

At least not obviously.

A narrow leather carrying harness with two adjustable side loops and broad back strap.

Condition card:

PORTER'S SIDE HARNESS

USED

STITCHING SOUND

ONE BUCKLE REPLACED

NO LOAD RATING REPRESENTED

Opening one copper.

I stared.

This was mundane.

Potentially useful.

Side loops could carry long object against torso while leaving hands free.

Crutches occupied hands.

Shoulder bag already did some of this badly.

Could carry cloth.

Could carry narrow cases.

Could maybe carry Three Reeds if padded, though artifact storage rules screamed.

Could carry groceries.

Could carry nothing and become expensive belt.

One copper.

No one bid.

I raised paddle.

"One, thirty-two."

Silence.

"Any two?"

A porter near front inspected it from seat.

Did not bid.

That mattered.

Maybe because used.

Maybe because no rating.

Maybe because he had better.

Caller said, "One once. One twice. Sold thirty-two."

I had bought harness.

Not magic.

I felt betrayed.

Payment clerk took one copper.

Cash nine.

She gave receipt and lot release tag.

I carried harness to inspection bench before leaving.

Same orange-sash clerk watched while I checked stitching more closely.

No hidden cuts.

Replacement buckle mismatched brass.

Leather dry but not cracked through.

"Can I put it on empty?"

"Yes."

I did.

Awkward with crutches.

Clerk held one strap only after I asked.

Harness sat diagonally across back/side.

One loop landed too low near left hip.

Adjustable.

I shortened.

Better.

Right side could hold narrow bundle without interfering with right crutch if object stayed behind elbow line.

Left side more complicated because residual limb and crutch path.

Could still use one-sided.

No load rating.

Important.

Not for heavy objects until someone competent checked it.

I removed.

"Do you know maker?"

Clerk looked at worn stamp.

"Looks like Heddel porter goods. Old mark. Could be copy."

"Any shop still services?"

"Heddel shop closed years ago. Leatherworker can assess stitching. Buckle standard enough."

Good.

No magical secret.

Just tool.

I left with harness over shoulder and nine copper.

Outside, bidder fourteen was loading draft-finder case into small handcart.

I tried not to look.

Failed.

She noticed.

"You wanted it."

"At three."

She smiled.

"At four too."

"Apparently not."

"Good."

I looked at her.

She tapped case.

"I'm buying six vanes, not array."

There.

Information.

"Replacement stock?"

"Maybe. Same ceramic profile as old archive humidity shutters. If tongues are wrong, I bought six pretty strips."

"You knew profile before bidding?"

"I could see it."

Fair.

"West Archive?"

"My father serviced stacks twenty years."

More information.

Market rational.

She looked at harness.

"You bought strap."

"Apparently."

"Useful."

"Less exciting."

"Useful often is."

She pushed cart away.

No name.

No subplot.

Just person who beat me because she knew more.

Excellent.

Survey man with echo pins passed next.

He didn't stop.

One pin in hand already.

He flicked glass head with fingernail.

It gave faint clear tone.

Other answered from box at his hip.

Not the second sale pin.

Compatible equipment.

Of course.

Four copper made even more sense.

I laughed.

He glanced back.

Kept walking.

At home Cala saw harness.

Looked behind me.

"That's all?"

"You sound disappointed."

"I'm checking for cylinder."

"There was cylinder."

Her face changed.

"Greg."

"I did not buy cylinder."

"Good."

Lyssa was at table again.

She held out hand.

"Give me harness."

"Why?"

"Because I sew."

Good reason.

I handed it over.

She bent leather.

Checked stitching.

Pulled replacement buckle.

"Dry."

"Not cracked through."

"Yet."

"Clerk said leatherworker assessment before load."

She nodded.

"One copper?"

"One."

"That's fine."

I felt absurdly pleased.

"I lost two auctions first."

"You didn't lose."

"They bought objects I wanted."

"Did you bid more than you wanted to pay?"

"No."

"Then you didn't lose."

I hated when people used my own logic against me.

Tavi came in.

"What is it?"

"Harness."

"Magic?"

"No."

He lost interest instantly.

Children were honest markets.

Later I took harness to leatherworker Cala knew two streets west.

Not a named recurring character.

He charged no inspection fee because it took less than minute and I did not ask him to repair.

"Stitching's fine for light carry," he said. "Leather wants oil. Don't trust that replacement buckle over maybe ten, twelve pounds until pin changed. Side loops weren't for heavy freight anyway. Documents, tools, narrow parcels."

"Can you replace pin?"

"Yes. Not today."

"Cost?"

"Depends pin. Come back if you care."

I cared later.

Not today.

At home I oiled leather with Cala's permission and cloth she gave me.

No artifact test.

No hidden glow.

No ancient rune appearing under dirt.

Leather became slightly darker.

That was all.

I tried harness empty again.

Then with rolled blanket.

Light.

Bulky enough to test geometry.

Right-side carry worked better than shoulder-bag strap improvisation.

Weight sat lower and closer to torso.

Crutch path clear if loop shortened.

Left-side carry interfered with left crutch during longer swing.

So one-sided.

Useful constraint.

I wrote:

CASH: 9 C

VALE: 23 C

RECOVERY SALE

DRAFT FINDER: STOPPED AT 3 / SOLD 4

ECHO PINS: STOPPED AT 3 / SOLD 4

PORTER HARNESS: 1 C

LIGHT CARRY ONLY UNTIL BUCKLE PIN CHECKED

RIGHT SIDE BETTER

Then I stared at first two lines.

Stopped at three.

Twice.

That felt better than buying either.

Not because objects were bad.

Draft finder might be excellent for woman who knew its vanes.

Echo pins were clearly worth more to man with compatible equipment.

Value was not inside object alone.

It lived between object and owner.

Dangerous sentence.

Sounded like philosophy.

I crossed it out before writing it.

No need.

I had nine copper, one useful harness, and no mysterious pressure cylinder leaking in bedroom.

Bidder.

Apparently restraint counted too.

# CHAPTER 369

## THE CARRIER

The harness became useful because Lyssa made too much cloth.

Not personally.

That would have been alarming.

She had finished Marra's first lot.

Twelve aprons.

Twelve sleeve pairs.

Eight carry wraps.

Which, when folded, became three bundles large enough to make the table look employed.

I stood beside them wearing the porter harness.

Lyssa looked at me.

Then the bundles.

Then me again.

"One."

"Harness has two side loops."

"One bundle."

"Hostile to equipment ambition."

That was unfair.

Mostly.

Cala was tying the second bundle with gray cord.

"Light carry," she said.

"Yes."

"Buckle not trusted past twelve pounds."

"Ten to twelve."

"That is not improvement."

"Precision is always improvement."

Lyssa put both hands on hips.

Charcoal coat hung from chair behind her.

She was not wearing it.

Work morning.

Old shirt, sleeves rolled, hair tied badly because she'd redone it twice and stopped caring.

Her fingers had two tiny red marks where needle had won arguments.

"You're carrying one," she said.

"Two side loops."

"One."

"Harness has ambitions."

"Harness can develop them slowly."

Cala nodded.

Household conspiracy.

I accepted.

One bundle.

Not the largest.

The eight carry wraps went together, narrow folded stack tied twice.

Cala lifted it with both hands and judged weight.

"Maybe six pounds."

Lyssa lifted too.

"Seven."

"How did you gain one pound by touching it?" I asked.

"Experience."

"Magic."

"Cloth."

We fitted bundle into right-side loop.

Not elegant.

Harness side loop had been made for documents, tools, narrow parcels.

This was a narrow parcel if generous with definition.

I shortened lower strap until bundle sat behind elbow line.

Crutch clear.

Upper edge pressed ribs lightly when I leaned left.

I adjusted again.

Better.

Lyssa crouched in front of me and pulled cord.

"Too loose."

"Harness strap or bundle tie?"

"Bundle."

She retied.

I watched because her hands were faster than mine and because bending over tied parcel while balancing on one leg and two crutches was possible but stupid.

There were many things I could do.

Useful category was smaller.

Tavi came downstairs.

Stopped.

"You're wearing cloth."

"Carrying."

"Looks like wearing."

"It's a harness."

"Still wearing."

He had me there.

He walked around once.

"Can I put sword in it?"

"No."

"What if wood sword?"

"No."

"What if spoon?"

"Why spoon?"

"Sword size."

I looked at Cala.

She was smiling into cord.

Traitor.

Lyssa lifted the other two bundles, one under each arm, then immediately put one back.

"That's stupid."

"Growth," I said.

"Don't."

She took largest apron bundle against chest and left sleeve pairs for Cala to carry to door.

Then reconsidered.

"Actually."

She looked at me.

I waited.

"Could second fit over left shoulder bag?"

"Probably."

"Safely?"

Better question.

I tested empty geometry with bundle held against bag.

No.

Left side made crutch swing tight.

"Not comfortably."

"Then no."

Easy.

No heroic cargo optimization.

We had bodies.

Bodies had limits.

We also had streets and hands and possibility of making two trips.

Cala said, "I'll walk to corner with third. You take it from there after you set first down at Marra's."

Lyssa looked at clock.

"That adds maybe ten minutes."

"Yes."

"Fine."

Nobody died from logistics.

A lesson civilization learned repeatedly.

We left after first bell.

I carried wraps on right harness.

Lyssa carried aprons.

Cala carried sleeves as far as East Cloth corner, where Jessa's younger cousin happened to be passing with empty handcart.

Not fate.

Carrow.

He knew Cala, knew Marra's green door, and offered cart corner-to-shop because he was going two doors past anyway.

Cala said yes before I could turn coincidence into philosophy.

Sleeve bundle went into cart.

No fee.

No debt.

Just someone going same way.

This city was dangerous that way.

You could start expecting people.

We walked.

The harness worked.

That was almost disappointing in simplicity.

No glow.

No hidden maker mark.

No old porter spirit waking because I bore honest labor.

Leather creaked.

Bundle stayed against right side.

Weight sat lower than shoulder bag.

My right crutch cleared.

On steeper curb I had to rotate torso slightly so front corner didn't bump thigh.

Fine.

One actual constraint.

One useful object.

I could live with that.

Lyssa glanced over twice.

Second time she said, "Stop looking pleased."

"I'm walking."

"Pleasedly."

"Not word."

"Is now."

She adjusted apron bundle higher.

"How are hands?" I asked.

"Fine."

"Actually?"

She flexed right thumb.

"Sore. Not bad."

"How long total?"

"Two long days. One short evening. Yesterday morning for finishing and checking."

"So maybe two and a half."

"Roughly."

"And you still like sewing?"

"I like some sewing."

"Important distinction."

"I don't like twelve identical apron ties."

"Shocking."

"By nine, I hated ties personally."

"What did ten do?"

"Apologized. Eleven was fine. Twelve got cocky again."

Messier when tired.

Good.

I liked it.

Not because every sentence needed performance.

Because it sounded like her after three days of cloth.

At Marra's green door, cousin's handcart arrived maybe half minute before us.

He had already set sleeve bundle just inside.

Jessa stood over it checking cord.

"You tied this?"

Lyssa said, "Yes."

"Ugly."

"Secure."

"Still ugly."

"Your face is ugly."

Jessa considered.

"Fair."

Greg remained gravitational center of dry comeback register in theory.

In practice, apparently everyone was allowed one.

I did not write that down.

Marra came from rear room wiping chalk from fingers.

She looked at three bundles.

Then at Lyssa.

"All?"

"All."

"Finished when?"

"Last wrap yesterday. Checked this morning."

"Any defects returned?"

"One apron front had a thin place near lower edge. I marked it and left unfinished like card said. It's in top of apron bundle."

Marra nodded once.

Good.

No praise yet.

Work first.

She pointed to long front table.

"Open by type."

Lyssa set aprons down.

Jessa brought sleeves.

I reached for harness bundle.

Marra said, "You can put that on floor first."

"Table offended?"

"Table clean. Street harness not."

Correct.

I lowered wrap bundle to clean board Marra put down.

Then removed harness.

No one applauded.

Trade remained cruel.

Marra started with aprons.

Not every piece.

First three.

Then random fifth, eighth, twelfth.

She checked seam width against marked strip.

Pulled ties.

Turned corners.

Ran thumb along hem.

Looked at one stitch line twice.

Lyssa stood with arms folded.

Not defensive.

Trying to look not defensive.

Different.

Jessa leaned against shelf until Marra said, "If you're going to hover, hover while sorting fasteners."

"I was observing history."

"History can sort fasteners."

Jessa sorted.

Marra checked sleeve pairs.

Four sets.

Then wrap bundle.

First.

Third.

Seventh.

She unfolded eighth completely.

Stopped.

Lyssa's shoulders tightened.

Marra touched closure loop at one end.

"This is old position."

Lyssa stepped forward.

"Position on marked cut."

"Yes."

Marra pulled work card from shelf.

Compared.

Then opened narrow message strip clipped behind it.

Read.

Her mouth changed.

Not much.

Enough.

"Hall changed loop offset yesterday."

Lyssa stared.

"Yesterday when?"

"Second bell."

"I finished wraps before midday."

"I know."

"Did you send change?"

"No."

"Why?"

Marra looked at strip.

"Because I read it, updated shop batch, and failed to remember your lot was already outside shop."

There.

Actual mistake.

No hidden test.

No cleverness.

Just work.

Lyssa looked down at wrap.

"How many wrong?"

"All eight if same."

"They're same."

"Then all eight."

Silence.

My brain immediately started building solutions.

Move loops.

How long?

Who pays?

Material damage?

Could maybe cut and resew.

Not my work.

I shut up.

Marra looked at Lyssa.

"My written-mark error category."

Lyssa exhaled through nose.

"Good."

Not happy.

Clear.

Marra said, "Your finishing is accepted. I own rework."

"You don't want me to move them?"

"Not under first-lot terms."

"I can."

"I know."

"If you pay it."

Marra's eyebrows moved.

There was Lyssa.

"Separate offer," Marra said.

"How much?"

Jessa stopped sorting fasteners without pretending not to listen.

Marra inspected one loop attachment more closely.

"Eight loops. Existing stitch holes may remain visible if moved. Hall spec says service goods, not presentation. I need to confirm whether patch line acceptable before anyone cuts thread."

Lyssa nodded.

"So not offer yet."

"Correct."

Good.

No invented number.

No automatic scope creep.

Marra set wrap aside.

Then continued inspection.

That mattered.

One problem did not turn whole lot into failure.

She found one sleeve cuff with a three-stitch section slightly tight.

"This yours."

Lyssa leaned close.

Pulled fabric gently.

"Yes."

"Would pass household. Not mine."

"Fine."

"Can fix here."

"How long?"

"Five minutes."

Lyssa looked at me.

Not for permission.

Just because I existed.

"You mind?"

"I am carrier."

"That wasn't answer."

"No."

She took sleeve pair to side bench.

Jessa handed her seam pick without comment.

Then said, "Don't bleed on it."

"I haven't."

"Yet."

"You are deeply supportive."

"Professionally."

Lyssa fixed three stitches.

Not dramatic.

Five minutes was generous.

Marra checked again.

Accepted.

Then she counted pieces against card.

Twelve aprons.

Twelve sleeve pairs.

Eight wraps.

One marked material defect unfinished.

Marra had supplied defect.

No penalty.

She wrote on card.

FIRST LOT RETURNED

FINISH ACCEPTED

1 MATERIAL DEFECT HELD

8 WRAPS REQUIRE CLIENT-SPEC LOOP REVISION

SHOP RESPONSIBILITY

Then she signed below.

Lyssa watched every word.

"Six?" she asked.

Marra looked up.

"Six."

No ceremony.

She went to cash drawer.

Counted six copper onto table.

One.

Two.

Three.

Four.

Five.

Six.

Lyssa looked at money.

Did not touch immediately.

Not because amount enormous.

Because money had been maybe for four days.

Now it was work already done.

Different thing.

Marra said, "Count it."

Lyssa did.

Six.

Into charcoal coat pocket she was not wearing.

No.

Wrong.

She had left coat home.

She used small purse from trouser pocket instead.

Important not to let coat teleport because convenient.

I would have laughed if anyone knew why.

Marra turned page in ledger.

"Hall confirmed second lot this morning."

Lyssa's hand stayed on purse.

"How large?"

"Sixteen aprons. Sixteen sleeve pairs. Twelve wraps. No heat-cloths yet."

"Rate?"

"Nine copper accepted return. Materials supplied. Five days."

Lyssa did not answer.

Good.

Marra continued before silence became pressure.

"Same error categories. No delivery penalty passed through. I update your card if Hall changes written spec. If I fail to transmit after lot leaves, that's mine."

The wrap mistake had already changed process.

Small.

Real.

Lyssa asked, "Any bench use included?"

"No. If you want it, separate."

"Do I need answer now?"

"Tomorrow midday."

Lyssa nodded.

"I'll answer tomorrow."

Marra nodded back.

No sales pitch.

No speech about future.

Work existed.

Offer existed.

Decision could wait.

Then Marra looked at eight wraps.

"If Hall accepts visible patch line, I'll offer you rework separately because you know your own stitches. If they require clean relocation, I may do it here."

"Fine."

"I'll send note."

"Fine."

Jessa said, "She means actually send note this time."

Marra looked at her.

Jessa held up both hands.

"Process improvement."

"Fasteners."

"History is sorting them."

Marra almost smiled.

Almost.

We left with less cloth and more money.

Not my money.

My harness was empty.

That felt wrong enough that Lyssa noticed.

"You look disappointed."

"Carrier without cargo."

"You carried."

"Past tense identity crisis."

"You'll survive."

Outside green door, she stopped under awning.

Rain had started lightly.

Not blue hour.

Just weather.

She took purse out.

Counted six again.

Then put it away.

"You don't have to tell me what you're thinking," I said.

She looked at me.

"That's new."

"Growth."

"You keep saying that and making it less true."

Fair.

We started home.

After half block she said, "Nine is better."

"More than six."

"Thank you, scholar."

"Available for advanced arithmetic."

"But second lot is bigger."

"Yes."

"Five days instead of four."

"Yes."

"First one took me about two and half days actual work. Maybe a little more if I count checking."

I waited.

This was her thinking out loud.

Not necessarily invitation.

"I was faster by end," she said. "Aprons got easy. Sleeves fine. Wraps boring."

"Mm."

She looked over.

"You can talk."

Permission.

"Nine for larger lot may still be better if pace holds. But larger quantity might eat more than extra day, and you don't know if repeating it every week makes you hate it."

"Yes."

"Also you now know Marra pays when work accepted."

"Important."

"And when her mark changes, she owned it."

"More important."

She walked several steps.

"I thought she'd try to make me move loops for free."

"She didn't."

"No."

"Would you have?"

"No."

Fast answer.

Then slower.

"Maybe first month ago I would have. Because I would've thought refusing meant I was difficult. Or not grateful. Or not good enough."

There.

Not speech polished for audience.

Thought still arriving while mouth moved.

"Now?" I asked.

"Now I did work she asked for. Correctly except three stupid stitches. She paid six. Her client changed thing after. That's not gratitude problem."

"No."

"And if she wants me to fix loops, she can pay me."

"Yes."

"Stop agreeing like priest."

"I can object."

"Don't."

We walked.

Rain strengthened enough for awnings to matter.

We took covered route along spice shops.

Harness straps darkened slightly where drops hit.

Oiled leather handled it fine.

No crisis.

At one crossing a delivery boy nearly backed into my empty right loop with crate corner.

I turned sideways.

He apologized without stopping.

Useful equipment also occupied space when empty.

Noted mentally.

No ledger needed.

At home Cala was at stove.

Tavi had spoon sword.

Of course.

He pointed at empty harness.

"Where cloth?"

"Delivered."

"Then why still wearing?"

I looked down.

Fair.

I removed it.

Cala looked at Lyssa.

Not purse.

Face.

"Accepted?"

"Yes."

"All?"

"My work, yes. One material defect. Hall changed wrap loop position after cut. Marra owns rework."

Cala nodded.

"Paid?"

Lyssa pulled purse and shook once.

"Six."

Tavi said, "Can I see?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Because it's money."

"I know what money looks like."

"Then you don't need to see mine."

He thought about this.

"Okay."

Surprisingly effective.

Cala asked, "More work?"

"Second lot offered. Nine copper. Bigger. Five days. Answer tomorrow."

Cala stirred pot.

"You want it?"

Lyssa opened mouth.

Stopped.

"I don't know yet."

Cala nodded.

"Then know tomorrow."

That was Cala.

No optimization model.

No speech.

Know tomorrow.

Lyssa sat.

I hung harness on chair back.

Not artifact chest.

It was leather.

Tavi touched one side loop.

"Spoon fits."

"No."

"You didn't test."

"No."

"That's not science."

I stared at him.

Someone had taught child wrong lessons.

Possibly me.

Lyssa laughed hard enough to bend forward.

Not elegant laugh.

Tired laugh.

She covered face.

"Don't encourage him," I said.

"I can't help it."

Cala said, "Spoon stays at table."

Tavi returned spoon.

Democracy restored.

Later, after dinner, Lyssa spread blank scrap beside work chest.

Not Marra's card.

Her own.

She wrote numbers.

I did not lean over.

This was advanced relationship technique.

She noticed anyway.

"You can look."

I moved closer.

FIRST LOT

6 C

2 B BENCH

ABOUT 2.5 DAYS WORK

3-STITCH FIX

MARra ERROR ON WRAPS

She crossed out lowercase mistake in Marra and rewrote.

Then below:

SECOND LOT

9 C

BIGGER

5 DAYS

NO BENCH INCLUDED

Then:

DO I WANT TO DO THIS EVERY WEEK?

She stared at last line.

"That's different question," I said.

"I know."

"Second lot doesn't necessarily mean every week."

"I know."

"Then why write it?"

She chewed inside cheek.

"Because if I say yes twice, people start thinking pattern. Including me."

Good answer.

"You can still stop after two."

"I know."

"You can also ask future weekly terms before third."

"I know, Greg."

There it was.

Too much.

I stopped.

She looked at me after moment.

"Sorry."

"No need."

"There is. You weren't doing anything wrong. I just have thread in brain."

Messy phrase.

Good phrase.

"Thread brain sounds terminal."

"Probably."

She tapped pencil against paper.

"I think I want second lot."

"Okay."

"That is all?"

"You said you think."

"I do."

"Then think until tomorrow."

She narrowed eyes.

"Cala infected you."

"Plan."

From kitchen Cala called, "I heard that."

Lyssa laughed again.

I went upstairs later and wrote my own notes.

Not Lyssa's six.

Not her nine maybe.

Mine.

CASH: 9 C

VALE: 23 C

PORTER HARNESS

RIGHT-SIDE LIGHT PARCEL: WORKS

EMPTY LOOP STILL CATCHES SPACE

BUCKLE PIN STILL NOT CHECKED FOR HEAVIER LOAD

Then I stopped.

Almost wrote about Marra accepting Lyssa's lot.

Did not.

Not my ledger.

Still mattered.

That was inconvenient.

Some of best things happening in my life refused to become my property, income, skill, or problem.

I was getting better at letting that happen.

Maybe.

Carrier.

Today that had been enough.

# CHAPTER 370

## THE STUDENT

Hessa found me teaching Tavi how not to fall off a chair.

This was less hypocritical than it sounds.

He was not on chair yet.

Important distinction.

The chair stood in middle of kitchen because Cala had asked him to wipe upper cabinet doors.

Tavi had interpreted this as permission to become architecture.

I had intercepted at chair plus footstool.

"One chair," I said.

"I can't reach."

"Then ask taller person."

"You are taller."

"Excellent."

"But you have crutches."

"Less excellent."

Lyssa, at table, did not look up from scrap where she had written Marra's second-lot numbers again.

"Ask me."

Tavi looked at her.

"You're busy."

"I can wipe cabinet."

"Cala told me."

There.

Work ownership.

Apparently everyone had contracts now.

"Then one chair," I said. "Flat. No stool. Move chair instead of reaching sideways."

"That takes longer."

"Falling also takes time."

"Not much."

"Recovery does."

He considered this with annoying seriousness.

Knock came at door.

Cala opened it.

Hessa stood outside.

Of course.

She looked past Cala at me.

Then at Tavi.

Then at chair.

"What are you teaching?"

"Gravity."

Tavi said, "Chair."

Hessa nodded.

"Chair is harder."

I disliked immediate alliance.

"Why are you here?"

"Shoes."

I looked down.

I had one.

"Cruel."

"Get ready."

No note.

No appointment.

No greeting worth keeping.

Hessa remained Hessa.

Lyssa looked up.

"Magic?"

"Probably," I said.

Hessa said, "Yes."

"See?"

Lyssa looked at her scrap again.

"I'm taking Marra's second lot."

There.

Resolved.

No council.

No ceremony.

"Nine?" I asked.

"Nine. Five days. Same terms. I am going after breakfast."

"Good."

She pointed pencil at me.

"That's all."

"I know."

"No face."

"I have to keep face."

"Neutral face."

I attempted.

She narrowed eyes.

"Worse. Go do magic."

Hessa had already stepped back outside.

Apparently dismissed by household.

I ate enough bread that Cala did not call me back.

No artifacts.

No porter harness.

No oval.

I left with crutches, ordinary shoulder bag, notebook, charcoal.

Hessa checked bag anyway.

"Still insulting," I said.

"Still useful."

We did not go to South Walk.

That was first warning.

We went east.

Past cloth lanes.

Past narrow square where two fish sellers were arguing over drain water.

Past a shrine wall with seven little brass cups set into stone.

Then down a lane I knew only as route toward old dye yards.

Most had moved farther south years ago.

Too much water use.

Too much colored runoff.

Too many neighbors discovering opinions about purple gutters.

One yard remained active.

Another had become storage.

Hessa turned into third.

Gate open.

No sign.

Inside, long brick court held three empty dye troughs, a roofed side gallery, and lines strung overhead between posts.

Hundreds of little pale cloth tabs hung from them.

Not laundry.

Practice markers.

Wind moved them.

Not much.

Enough.

I stopped.

"No."

Hessa kept walking.

"Good morning to you too."

"Moving targets?"

"No."

I looked at tabs.

They moved.

"Those are philosophically moving."

"They are wind markers."

Ah.

Different problem.

Hessa set small wooden case on dry trough edge.

"Body."

"Fine."

She waited.

"Right leg fine. Left knee fine. Shoulders slightly tired from yesterday carrying cloth. Hands fine. Sleep good."

"Magic since last session?"

"No."

"Artifacts?"

"Three Reeds once for neighbor emberwings. Short activation. No mana from me."

"Anything else?"

"No."

"Work?"

"Theatre route instruction. Auction. Carried Lyssa's work yesterday."

"Fall?"

"No."

"Near fall?"

I thought about auction.

No.

Harness curb.

No.

"No."

Hessa nodded.

Then pointed to a chalk path already drawn through court.

Not straight.

It bent around first empty trough, narrowed between two posts, widened near back wall, then returned along other side.

A loop.

"Walk."

I walked.

No mana.

First turn easy.

Narrow post gap required shorter crutch placement.

Back wall had rough brick seam that made right-foot landing slightly uneven.

Return side broad.

I completed.

"Again."

Second.

"Again."

Third.

I stopped at start.

"You changed route."

"Yes."

"Last moving-caster result was familiar straight route."

"Yes."

"So today is route complexity."

"No."

I hated her.

"Then why route?"

"Because you keep trying to name lesson before taking it."

That landed harder than deserved.

Probably because true.

I had spent first life learning fast partly by predicting instructors.

By thirty, that was useful.

By fifty, most people teaching me either had narrow specialty I wanted or were wasting my time.

Current life had apparently decided to fix personality defect by making me nineteen and Bronze.

Hessa opened wooden case.

One bead.

Same size as before.

Dark blue this time.

She did not hang it.

She set it on narrow stone shelf built into back wall.

Stationary.

Farther from route than prior South Walk bead.

Not much.

Maybe one pace.

There.

Distance axis.

I said nothing.

Hessa looked at me.

"Good."

"I hate praise for silence."

"Then stop earning it."

I almost answered.

Did not.

Worse.

She pointed to start.

"Six minimal. Walking full loop. Draw only on broad return side."

"Why broad return?"

"Because I said."

Not satisfying.

Enough.

Attempt one.

I walked first bend.

Shortened through posts.

Right foot over brick seam.

Return side.

Both crutches planted.

Minimal draw.

Clean.

Released before turn.

Attempt two.

Clean enough.

Three smeared because I drew while right crutch was still settling.

Four clean.

Five broke early.

Six clean.

Hessa marked nothing visible.

"Count."

"Fifty-nine minimal."

"Good."

Six added to fifty-three.

No miracle.

Body remembered enough.

"Again," she said.

"Minimal?"

"No mana."

Of course.

I walked loop twice with no draw.

Hessa moved from trough to side gallery.

Then told me to walk again.

I did.

At broad return she said, "Where is bead?"

I looked.

Back wall.

"There."

"You looked."

"You asked."

"Again."

I walked.

"Where is bead?"

This time I kept eyes on route.

"Back wall, left of brick seam, about one pace farther than old near range."

"Better."

"You don't want visual correction."

"I want you to stop hunting target with head every pass."

Different.

"Peripheral?"

"Enough to know where it is. Not enough to rebuild route around it."

There.

The cloth tabs overhead shifted with breeze.

One line moved left to right.

Another barely stirred.

Wind markers.

Not target.

They told me court air changed between passes.

Potential noise.

Hessa had chosen outdoor yard deliberately.

I almost said that.

Then remembered lesson about naming lesson.

Student.

Annoying role.

She pointed at start.

"Four external at old distance first."

I looked at bead.

"It's farther."

Hessa walked to wall, picked bead up, moved it to short iron peg closer to route.

Old near range approximately.

Baseline.

Good.

I said nothing.

"You can stop performing silence," she said.

"You created this."

"Walk."

Attempt one.

Full loop.

No draw until broad return.

Target entered peripheral field.

I initiated after right-foot contact.

Tiny effect.

Bead clicked against peg.

Late enough that I felt shoulder chase.

Hessa said, "No."

Attempt two.

Nothing.

Attempt three.

Small clean movement.

No shoulder correction.

Still moving.

Attempt four.

Another movement, uglier.

Hessa stopped me.

"One clean enough."

"Three moved."

"One clean enough."

"I know."

"You said three."

"I was mourning."

She moved bead back to wall shelf.

Farther.

"Now twelve."

There.

Distance axis.

Same moving caster.

Stationary target.

Same route after repeated familiarization today.

One variable stretched.

Mostly.

Outdoor air still existed.

Hessa pointed up.

"Tabs."

"Wind."

"If lower line jumps, skip pass."

"Because cross-draft changes bead?"

"Because I want less noise."

Good.

Attempt one.

I walked.

Lower tabs shifted hard near return.

Skipped.

"Not counted," Hessa said.

Attempt one actual.

Draw.

Nothing.

Distance was not dramatic.

Mana did not care about my feelings.

Attempt two.

Nothing.

Three.

Tiny bead movement.

Too late.

My torso had already passed clean geometry and I reached mentally backward.

Hessa said nothing.

Worse.

Attempt four.

I tried earlier.

Nothing.

Five.

Skipped because wind tabs jumped.

Not counted.

Five actual.

I felt shape settle during broad return.

Held target location without staring.

External push.

Bead moved.

Small.

Clean enough that I knew before Hessa spoke.

"Keep walking."

I did.

No smile.

Mostly.

At start she said, "One."

"One."

"Do not reproduce it."

"You say that like malicious curse."

"It works."

Attempt six failed because I reproduced it.

I swore.

Hessa did not smile.

Her eyes did.

Attempt seven.

Nothing.

Eight.

Bead moved, but wind line had started shifting just before effect.

Hessa said, "Discard."

"It moved before big gust."

"Discard."

"You enjoy waste."

"I enjoy not lying."

Fair.

Attempt eight still counted as attempt.

Result discarded as evidence.

Nine.

No effect.

Ten.

Tiny clean movement.

Not as much as five.

But timing clean.

No wind jump.

No late shoulder.

Hessa raised hand.

Stop.

"That is ten," I said.

"Yes."

"You said twelve."

"I changed mind."

"There are two left."

"No."

"Why?"

"Because I have two clean-enough farther results and you are beginning to chase success."

I opened mouth.

Closed.

She was right.

Worse.

"So ten counted farther attempts."

"Yes."

"Two clean enough."

"Yes."

"One other moved late. One moved under wind noise and gets discarded as evidence."

"Yes."

"Four baseline attempts first, one clean enough."

"Yes."

"External total becomes one hundred twenty-nine."

Hessa paused.

I counted again.

One hundred fifteen before today.

Four near baseline.

Ten farther.

Fourteen.

One hundred twenty-nine.

"Correct," she said.

"Minimal fifty-nine. Shaping fifty-three."

"Correct."

I leaned on crutches.

Not exhausted.

Hands slightly warm from route repetition.

Shoulders working.

Brain louder.

"So distance improved."

Hessa looked at me.

I sighed.

"No."

"Try again."

"Today, on this route, after familiarization, I produced two clean-enough small external effects on a stationary target about one pace farther than prior near-range moving-caster work. Two out of ten counted farther attempts."

"Better."

"One clean baseline out of four at old distance."

"Yes."

"Which means I was not especially reliable even before distance changed."

"Yes."

"You could say something encouraging."

"You noticed."

That was apparently encouragement.

I sat on dry trough edge.

Hessa gave me water.

I drank.

Overhead tabs fluttered.

"Why this yard?" I asked.

"Available."

I stared.

"That's all?"

"No."

Of course.

She pointed to tabs.

"South Walk is too clean."

There.

"You wanted environmental noise visible."

"Some."

"But not moving target."

"Correct."

"And route has turns so I cannot make one perfect straight-line timing ritual."

"Partly."

"What else?"

"You tell me."

Trap.

I looked at court.

Three troughs.

Narrow posts.

Rough seam.

Broad return.

Target farther.

Wind visible.

No crowd.

No real consequence if effect failed.

"It makes me move like I actually move. Not marching lane."

Hessa nodded once.

"Closer."

"Current body route changes. Crutch placement changes with width and surface. You want effect to survive some ordinary geometry without adding target movement or speed increase yet."

"Yes."

There.

I felt pleased.

Hessa ruined it.

"But I did not count that as separate variable."

"Why not?"

"Because today is not proof of route-general skill. You learned this loop first."

Right.

"So this is still one practiced route."

"Yes."

"Just less artificial than South Walk."

"Yes."

"You are deeply committed to making every result smaller."

"Small results fit through doors."

That sounded like something Hessa had been waiting years to say.

"Was that metaphor?"

"No."

"It was."

"Drink water."

I drank.

She packed bead.

I watched hands.

Young hands.

Competent.

That still did something strange to me sometimes.

Not because Hessa was young exactly.

I was young now too.

That remained rude.

In first life I had taught people older than her.

Fought beside people younger.

Taken orders from specialists who knew things I didn't.

Age had never made expertise simple.

But being nineteen again put old reflex in wrong room.

I wanted to skip explanation because I remembered categories.

Wanted to infer next drill because I knew training structures.

Wanted to turn every correction into system before system had enough evidence.

Hessa kept making me do stupid thing instead.

Be student.

Not pretend ignorance.

Not erase first life.

Just let somebody else own lesson when it was theirs.

Annoying.

Probably useful.

"Why are you making face?" Hessa asked.

"Personal growth."

"Stop."

Reasonable.

She closed case.

"No independent moving external work."

"Yes."

"No farther target practice alone."

"Yes."

"No artifact combination."

"Yes."

"No using Three Reeds to create controlled visual field for casting."

I looked at her.

"I hadn't thought of that."

"Now you have."

"This is your fault."

"Still no."

"Cruel."

"Useful."

We left dye yard.

At gate I asked, "Next axis speed?"

Hessa kept walking.

"Maybe."

"Distance again?"

"Maybe."

"Stationary casting at farther range to separate movement cost?"

She looked at me.

That one apparently less stupid.

"Maybe."

"You have three maybes."

"I have more."

"Can I have one?"

"No."

Teacher.

Terrible species.

We split near East Cloth.

Hessa went north without goodbye.

I went toward Marra's green door because Lyssa had said she was answering after breakfast.

Not to supervise.

Mostly because route home passed within half block.

I saw her before I reached door.

She came out carrying one folded work card and no cloth.

"You took it," I said.

"I said I would."

"You said you thought you would."

"Now I did."

"Nine copper. Five days."

"Yes."

"Same categories?"

She gave me look.

I stopped.

"Sorry."

"Thank you."

We walked together.

After ten steps she said, "Yes, same categories. Marra wrote client-update line onto card this time."

"Good."

"I know."

"Any wrap rework?"

"Hall accepts visible patch line. Marra offered me one copper to move all eight loops. I said yes. Separate from second lot. I can do it this afternoon."

There.

New money.

Her money.

New work.

Her work.

"Good," I said.

She pointed at me without looking.

"One good. That's all."

"Understood."

"How was magic?"

"Hessa made me student."

"Were you not already?"

"Hostile question."

"Did anything move?"

"Yes."

"That sounds good."

"Careful. Hessa might hear."

Lyssa looked around.

"Is she in wall?"

"Tavi believes possible."

"Then definitely."

At home Tavi had cleaned cabinet doors.

Mostly.

One had a wet streak shaped like hand.

Cala inspected.

"You missed top left."

Tavi looked at chair.

Then at me.

Then at Lyssa.

"Can someone tall do it?"

I pointed at him.

"Learning."

Cala handed cloth to Lyssa.

Lyssa wiped top left in two seconds.

Tavi looked offended by efficiency.

"I could've done that."

"You did rest," Cala said.

"Most."

"Most is why we're here."

He accepted this with visible reluctance.

I went upstairs.

No money movement for me.

Nine copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

I wrote:

59 MINIMAL

53 DELIBERATE SHAPING

129 EXTERNAL EFFECT

MOVING CASTER / PRACTICED LOOP / STATIONARY TARGET

OLD NEAR RANGE: 4 ATTEMPTS / 1 CLEAN ENOUGH

FARTHER BY ABOUT 1 PACE: 10 COUNTED ATTEMPTS / 2 CLEAN ENOUGH

1 LATE MOVEMENT

1 MOVEMENT UNDER WIND NOISE: DISCARD AS EVIDENCE

WIND-JUMP PASSES SKIPPED / NOT COUNTED

Then:

DO NOT GENERALIZE TO MOVING TARGET / FASTER ROUTE / UNFAMILIAR ROUTE / UNSUPERVISED USE

Then I almost wrote:

I KNOW WHAT HESSA IS DOING.

I stopped.

Crossed blank space where sentence would have gone.

That was old habit.

Knowing shape of lesson before lesson finished.

Sometimes useful.

Sometimes just another way of trying to stand where teacher stood.

I wrote instead:

NEXT: HESSA DECIDES.

Stared at it.

Hated it.

Left it.

Student.

Apparently restraint counted there too.

# CHAPTER 371

## THE WITNESS

The dead man was arguing with a fishmonger.

Loudly.

This would have been more concerning if he were actually dead.

He was painted.

Mostly.

His face occupied a whitewashed wall across from East Fish Row, twelve feet tall and deeply offended by the price of silverfin.

The fishmonger painted beside him was equally offended by existence.

I stopped in middle of street.

Someone behind me said, "Move."

Reasonable.

I moved.

Then stopped again under awning.

The wall had not been like that yesterday.

I was almost certain.

Carrow changed quickly, but twelve-foot dead merchants usually left some procedural residue.

A woman beside me carrying two baskets said, "That's Pell."

"Which one?"

"Dead one."

Useful.

"How long?"

"Dead?"

"Painted."

"Morning."

She kept walking.

No explanation.

Carrow had standards.

I looked again.

The painted man had one hand raised with two fingers extended.

The painted fishmonger held a silverfin by tail.

Between them, in narrow black letters:

TWO BITS IS THEFT IF FISH STILL HAS EYES

Below that, in different hand:

PELL SAID THIS EVERY MARKET DAY FOR NINETEEN YEARS

Then:

HE WAS WRONG FOR EIGHTEEN OF THEM

People were laughing.

Not crowd exactly.

Current.

They slowed.

Read.

Pointed.

Moved on.

An old man touched wall with two fingers and kept walking.

A girl maybe ten asked mother, "Who was Pell?"

Mother said, "Annoying."

Then after three steps:

"Your grandfather liked him."

That was all.

I had somewhere to be.

Not urgently.

No guaranteed work.

No Hessa.

No auction.

No cloth to carry.

Nine copper in purse.

Twenty-three with Vale.

Apparently enough economic stability to become vulnerable to murals.

I stayed.

A boy on ladder was adding blue shadow beneath silverfin.

Not boy.

Young man.

Hard to tell from below.

He wore paint on both elbows and one cheek.

A woman on ground held ladder and shouted, "Too dark."

"It's shade."

"It's fish."

"Fish have shade."

"Not blue."

"This one does."

"Pell would complain."

"Pell complained about everything."

"Then honor him."

The painter sighed and wiped some blue away.

I liked city immediately more.

Then a bell rang farther down row.

Not alarm.

One clean strike.

People near wall turned.

A cart rolled slowly into street.

No goods.

Flowers.

Bad flowers.

Not ugly.

Old.

Stems tied in uneven bundles, some already browning.

Three children pulled cart with rope while two adults walked behind.

No coffin.

No body.

Just flowers, a folded coat, one wooden market scale, and a black board with white letters.

PELL ARVEN

FISH ROW / EAST GATE / EVERYWHERE HE WASN'T ASKED

DIED FOUR DAYS AGO

WALL WALK TODAY

SAY WHAT YOU REMEMBER

DO NOT IMPROVE HIM

That last line did work.

People laughed softer.

The cart stopped at mural.

Someone put folded coat beneath painted feet.

Not permanently, probably.

One of adults was fishmonger from mural.

Older now.

Real.

Same long nose.

Same narrow shoulders.

He looked at painted version of himself.

"Too handsome," he said.

Ladder painter looked down.

"Which one?"

"Both."

People laughed.

Real fishmonger touched painted Pell's raised fingers.

Then said, "Two bits wasn't theft."

Someone behind him shouted, "It was that day."

"Shut up, Laro."

"Dead man can't save you."

"Never did alive."

More laughter.

Then quiet.

Not commanded.

Arrived.

Fishmonger looked at wall.

"He bought from me every fifth day unless tide was bad. Complained about price before asking price. If I lowered it, complained fish was too small. If fish was big, complained bones were greedy. Once brought own knife and tried to show me how to cut my own fish."

A woman said, "That sounds like him."

"I threw knife in gutter."

"Also sounds like you."

"Yes."

He rubbed mouth.

"When my wife was sick, he brought soup every night and told everyone it was because I couldn't cook. Which was true. He never once said it was for her."

Silence again.

Then fishmonger added, "Soup was bad."

Relief.

People laughed.

He stepped aside.

Someone wrote on wall beneath black text.

SOUP BAD

No signature.

I understood now.

Wall walk.

Not funeral exactly.

Memory route.

Maybe temporary.

Maybe old Carrow custom I had somehow missed.

First life had taught me dozens of death customs.

Military names read from rolls.

River stones.

Ash lanterns.

Weapons broken or passed down.

Three kingdoms where you never spoke dead person's name for a year.

One where family hired professional liar to praise them so badly everyone could safely complain.

I had thought that one stupid until now.

This was better.

DO NOT IMPROVE HIM.

Hard instruction.

People loved improving dead.

Made them easier to carry.

Cart moved after maybe ten minutes.

So did crowd.

Not all.

Some stayed.

Some joined.

I joined.

No reason.

That was reason enough.

We went down East Fish Row.

Children pulled cart badly.

One rope longer than other, so cart kept drifting right.

Adult corrected twice.

Third time let them figure it out.

They did eventually.

At first stop, old cooper door.

Another painting waited.

Smaller.

Pell sitting on barrel with boot up, apparently telling cooper something unbearable.

Text:

HE KNEW BARRELS BECAUSE HE ONCE OWNED ONE

Someone had added:

IT LEAKED

Cooper came out.

Not old.

Middle-aged woman with shaved left side of head and sawdust on apron.

"That barrel did not leak," she said.

Three people shouted variations of yes it did.

She pointed at crowd.

"The floor leaked."

"Floors don't leak," fishmonger said.

"His did."

"Because barrel leaked."

"No evidence."

This city had infected everyone.

She looked at folded coat on cart.

Face changed.

"He owed my father four copper for six years."

A man behind me said, "Seven."

"Six."

"He borrowed before flood year."

"After."

"Before."

Cooper looked at him.

"You want speak or count?"

He shut up.

"Four copper," she continued. "Then one morning he paid five and said interest was for making him feel guilty every time he walked past. My father said guilt was free. Pell said not if done professionally."

Fishmonger snorted.

Cooper touched cart scale.

"He was a pain. He fixed east gate tally when clerk shorted my father three deliveries. Wouldn't stop until they checked old board. Took two weeks. Nobody asked him."

She stepped back.

Someone wrote:

DEBT PAID LATE

Another hand added:

WITH INTEREST

Cooper immediately crossed out WITH INTEREST and wrote:

WITH ONE EXTRA COPPER AND A SPEECH

Better.

Accuracy mattered even in grief.

Maybe especially.

I followed.

Third stop was tiny shrine court between bakery and cobbler.

No mural.

Only blank gray board and chalk.

A priest with bad knees sat on stool.

He looked at cart.

"Late."

One child said, "Rope problem."

Priest nodded like this explained death.

"Pell never came inside," he said.

Someone laughed.

"He stood at door every first rain and argued about roof fund."

"Did he pay?" fishmonger asked.

"Eventually."

"How eventually?"

"After explaining how I should run shrine accounts."

"Did he know shrine accounts?"

"No."

"Did that stop him?"

Priest stared.

Crowd laughed.

He smiled.

"He carried my stool home during flood because I could not walk far. Then complained stool was heavier than faith."

Someone wrote it.

STOOL HEAVIER THAN FAITH

Priest said, "Don't write that."

Too late.

He sighed.

"Fine."

We moved.

I began noticing people who did not speak.

Woman with baskets from first wall followed three stops, never said anything.

A dock worker touched folded coat once.

Two boys joined because crowd was interesting, then left when memory required patience.

A man at spice stall watched from doorway and did not come out.

At fourth stop, he was already there.

Nobody explained.

Pell had routes.

That was what I understood.

Not importance exactly.

Not rank.

Routes.

He had irritated same fishmonger nineteen years.

Owed cooper family money.

Argued shrine roof.

Bought bread somewhere.

Apparently cheated at tiles.

Knew whose daughter moved north.

Forgot birthdays.

Remembered injuries.

Told same story too often.

Walked East Gate even when South Gate shorter because he hated one paving stone.

People knew him unevenly.

That was the shape.

No one had whole Pell.

Good.

Whole people were suspicious.

At bakery stop, a woman with flour to elbows said, "He stole rolls."

Fishmonger said, "Bought?"

"Stole."

"Pell had money."

"Still stole."

"Why?"

"Said stolen bread tasted better."

"Did it?"

"He paid after."

"Then not stolen."

Baker slammed palm on counter.

"That is what I told him."

Crowd lost itself for while.

I laughed too.

Harder than deserved.

Maybe because I had spent morning having Hessa reduce magic into ratios.

Maybe because yesterday I carried cloth and watched work become money that wasn't mine.

Maybe because dead man had apparently spent decades turning minor commerce into argument and people missed him for it.

I did not know Pell.

That became important.

At fifth stop, someone handed me chalk.

I looked at it.

"No."

Woman holding chalk frowned.

"You walking."

"Didn't know him."

"Write what you saw today."

"That's not memory."

"You saw wall."

"Of him painted."

"Still saw."

I shook head.

"No."

She shrugged and gave chalk to someone else.

No pressure.

Good.

I stayed.

Witness did not mean contributor.

Sometimes it meant not pretending.

That should have been obvious.

It wasn't always.

We turned onto Narrow Bell.

Here street tightened between old three-story houses.

Laundry overhead.

Cart barely fit around vegetable stand.

One child pulling rope said, "Left."

Other said, "I am left."

"Your other left."

"I only have one."

Adult closed eyes.

I considered helping.

Did not.

They solved it by moving vegetable basket six inches.

Civilization again.

At Narrow Bell, mural was different.

No Pell face.

Only pair of shoes painted beside door.

One upright.

One on side.

Text:

HE WAITED HERE THREE HOURS IN RAIN

Below:

BECAUSE MIRA SAID SHE WOULD COME BACK

No joke.

Crowd quiet before cart stopped.

A very old woman sat just inside doorway.

Blanket over knees despite warm day.

Mira, presumably.

She looked at painted shoes.

"Four hours," she said.

Someone near wall changed THREE to FOUR.

No ceremony.

Accuracy.

Mira said, "I was giving birth."

That changed context substantially.

"Not his," she added.

Crowd laughed, including her.

"My husband was upriver. Pell was supposed to fetch midwife. He fetched midwife, then stayed outside because I told him if he came in I'd kill him."

Fishmonger said, "Reasonable."

"He kept asking through door if I needed water."

"Did you?"

"Yes."

"Did you tell him?"

"No."

"Why?"

Mira looked at fishmonger.

"I was busy."

Fair.

She rubbed blanket edge.

"He came back next day with pears. Baby couldn't eat pears."

Someone laughed softly.

"I ate them."

A child pulling cart asked, "Where's baby?"

Mira pointed into crowd.

A woman maybe forty raised hand.

Oh.

That got me.

Not dramatically.

Small internal shift.

The daughter had been there whole time.

She looked nothing like story's baby because babies were temporary.

Annoying fact.

Mira said, "He taught her cards when she was six."

Daughter said, "Five."

"Six."

"Five."

"You couldn't count at five."

"I could count cards."

"Because Pell cheated."

"Exactly."

Someone wrote:

PEARS

Then underneath:

BAD AT TIMING

Mira pointed.

"Keep pears. Remove timing."

They did.

I stood near back.

No one knew me.

A few recognized face maybe from Senna portrait or theatre or simply city accumulation.

Nobody asked why I was there.

That felt right.

Procession continued toward East Gate.

By then maybe fifty people moved with it in pieces.

Never fifty together.

Some joined one block.

Some peeled away.

A butcher came out, shouted "He hated my sausage because he had no taste," then went back inside before anyone answered.

Someone wrote NO TASTE on cart board.

One of children crossed it out because instruction was say what you remember, not editorialize.

Good child.

At East Gate there was no wall painting.

There was old paving stone.

Cracked diagonal.

Apparently the one Pell hated.

Of course.

Cart stopped beside it.

Gate clerk came down from booth.

Different clerk than any I knew.

He looked at stone.

"He filed eleven complaints."

Fishmonger said, "Only eleven?"

"Written."

"Ah."

"First three said trip hazard. Next four said drainage. Then two said wheel catch. One said civic embarrassment. Last said previous ten complaints were evidence of institutional stubbornness."

I liked Pell more every minute.

"Was stone dangerous?" someone asked.

Clerk looked down.

"Not really."

Crowd laughed.

"It catches narrow wheels if you hit wrong angle," clerk admitted.

More laughter.

"We're replacing it next resurfacing."

Fishmonger shouted, "Don't tell him now."

Quiet came again.

Because there was no him to tell.

That was grief's cheap trick.

Every joke eventually found edge.

Gate clerk took folded coat from cart.

Held it up.

Brown.

Patched elbows.

One button missing.

"Who gets this?"

Nobody answered immediately.

Then Mira's daughter said, "Ralo."

Fishmonger said, "Too small."

"He'll wear it open."

"Ralo hates brown."

From back someone shouted, "I do."

Apparently Ralo.

"Then sell it," daughter said.

"Pell would haunt me."

Priest said, "He is not authorized."

Crowd laughed.

Coat went to Ralo anyway.

Scale went to fishmonger.

He protested.

Everyone ignored him.

Flowers got divided.

Not by family.

By route.

One bundle to fish stall.

One shrine.

One cooper.

One bakery.

One Narrow Bell door.

Children each took one.

Remaining browning stems went into East Gate water trough because nobody had plan and apparently Pell would have complained about waste.

Then it was over.

No final speech.

No priest blessing.

No bell beyond one strike.

People left.

I stayed near cracked stone.

Fishmonger stood beside me with Pell's scale under arm.

"You knew him?" he asked.

"No."

"Then why walk?"

Good question.

"I wanted to see."

He nodded.

Not offended.

"Now you did."

"Yes."

He looked at scale.

"Thing lies by half ounce."

"Why keep it?"

"Because he accused mine of lying for nineteen years."

"Does yours?"

He looked at me.

"Of course."

I laughed.

He left.

I walked home alone.

No chalk note from me.

No Pell story.

I had none.

But city had changed slightly.

Not because important man died.

Maybe Pell had been important to someone.

Everyone was.

That wasn't it.

A route had lost one repeated body.

Fish Row would still sell fish.

Cooper would make barrels.

Shrine roof fund would survive without argument.

East Gate stone would eventually be replaced and nobody would get to tell Pell he had technically won.

People would keep saying things he said.

Wrongly, probably.

Then someone would correct them.

Four hours, not three.

Five years old, not six.

One extra copper, not interest.

Soup bad.

That was life after someone too.

Correction.

I had died once.

This was not new information.

Still, first death had been different.

I had not attended aftermath.

Obviously.

I knew what I remembered.

Not what other people did.

Maybe someone improved me.

Probably.

Maybe someone made me worse.

Also probably.

Maybe there had been one stupid story about boots or soup or a door I always used.

I would never know.

That thought did not hurt exactly.

It had nowhere useful to go.

So I let it walk beside me.

At home Lyssa sat by window moving wrap loops.

Eight wraps stacked left.

Three finished on right.

Separate one-copper rework.

Her work.

She looked up.

"Where were you?"

"Funeral."

Needle stopped.

"Who died?"

"Pell."

"Pell who?"

"Exactly."

She stared.

"That was terrible answer."

"I didn't know him."

"Then why funeral?"

"Wall walk."

Recognition.

"Oh."

"You know it?"

"I've seen one. Not since I was little."

There.

Local life continuing to exist without me.

"Is it always like that?"

"Like what?"

"People correcting dead man."

She smiled.

"If people liked him."

"What if they didn't?"

"Probably shorter walk."

Reasonable.

She returned to stitching.

"What did he do?"

"Complained about fish. Owed cooper money. Hated paving stone. Brought pears to newborn."

"Sounds exhausting."

"Apparently."

"You would've liked him."

"Probably not."

"Exactly."

I sat.

Tavi came in carrying piece of charcoal.

"Why funeral?"

He had heard one word from hallway and arrived immediately.

"Man died."

"Old?"

"I don't know."

"How?"

"I don't know."

"Did you see body?"

"No."

"Then how know?"

"People told me."

He frowned.

"Could be trick."

Lyssa said, "Why would whole street trick Greg about dead man?"

Tavi thought seriously.

"Surprise party."

I looked at Lyssa.
