She put needle down because laughing with needle was apparently poor craft.

"For Pell?" I asked.

"Maybe he's not dead and they surprise him because he thinks funeral is for someone else."

"That is terrible surprise."

"Big surprise."

Technically.

Cala entered carrying folded laundry.

"Pell Arven?"

I turned.

"You knew him?"

"No. Saw board this morning."

"You didn't mention it."

"You didn't ask."

Cala remained undefeated.

"Did you know wall walk?"

"Yes."

"Why does nobody tell me city customs before I trip over them?"

She put laundry down.

"Too many."

Fair.

Lyssa resumed loop.

Tavi asked if we could paint him on wall when he died.

Cala said, "Not soon."

"Later."

"Later."

"Big?"

"Depends what people remember."

He looked at me.

"What would they paint you?"

Cruel question from child.

I considered crutches.

Artifacts.

Theatre.

Vale.

Bad decisions.

Good decisions delayed until they became bad differently.

"Probably arguing with sign."

Lyssa said, "Accurate."

Tavi said, "With sword."

"No sword."

"Big spoon."

"Absolutely not."

"Then no one remembers you."

Harsh.

He left.

I watched Lyssa stitch.

Three finished wraps became four.

Nothing dramatic.

One copper when done and accepted.

Second lot waiting after.

Life continuing around death because it had terrible manners.

Later upstairs I opened notes.

Nine copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Magic fifty-nine, fifty-three, one twenty-nine.

No changes.

I almost wrote WALL WALK.

Stopped.

Not because it didn't matter.

Because not everything that mattered needed to become system.

I had not known Pell.

I had not helped.

I had not fixed anything.

I had not earned or spent.

I had not learned useful spell.

I had watched people refuse to turn dead man into better person than he had been.

That felt like something.

Witness.

Maybe that was enough.

I closed notebook without writing it.

# CHAPTER 372

## THE GUIDE

Naro arrived with a jar full of bad decisions.

The jar clicked.

I looked at it.

He held it farther from body.

Good instinct.

"What is that?"

"I don't know."

"Excellent start."

"Mevi says don't shake it."

"Better."

"I shook it before she said."

There it was.

I was sitting at kitchen table oiling porter harness because leather had started feeling dry at one edge.

No load.

No experiment.

Maintenance.

Lyssa occupied window with second-lot apron pieces arranged in stacks that looked organized to her and hostile to everyone else.

Cala was cutting onions.

Tavi was supposed to be shelling peas.

He had shelled six and arranged them by size.

Productivity had become taxonomy.

Naro stood in doorway holding jar wrapped in dishcloth.

Behind him Mevi looked tired enough to have raised him personally from infancy despite being thirteen.

"He found it under west drain," she said.

"I didn't find it under drain."

"You put hand under drain and pulled it out."

"That's finding near drain."

"Under."

"Near."

I held hand up.

"Jar."

Naro set it carefully on table.

Cala moved onions away.

Lyssa moved cloth farther away without looking up.

Household competence.

The jar was ordinary thick glass.

Palm high.

Wire clasp.

Cloudy lid seal.

Inside were seven things that looked like black seeds.

Maybe eight.

They moved when I wasn't looking directly at them.

No.

That sentence was stupid.

I looked directly.

Seven.

One rolled slowly against glass.

Clicked.

Then stopped.

"Did you put them in jar?"

Naro shook head.

"Found like that."

"Where exactly?"

"West drain."

Mevi looked at him.

"Under west drain."

"Fine. Under."

"Which west drain?"

"Behind dye lane."

That narrowed nothing.

Carrow had many dye lanes because apparently clothing required infrastructure.

Mevi said, "The one by old red wall where water goes into covered channel before Narrow Bell."

I knew it.

Not far from yesterday's wall walk route.

Different problem.

Good.

"Why bring it here?"

Naro said, "Because you have weird things."

Cala made tiny sound that might have been laugh.

I looked at her.

No support.

"I do not operate municipal lost-and-found for weird things."

"You knew moth lamp."

"Shade unit."

"Dark lamp."

We had lost that war.

Mevi said, "I told him take it to drain keeper. He said Greg first because if dangerous you'd know."

"That is not true."

Naro looked genuinely surprised.

Important educational opportunity.

"I know some dangerous things," I said. "I do not know every dangerous thing. Nobody does."

"Hessa?"

"Especially not Hessa."

Somewhere, wall cracked.

Lyssa said, "She heard that."

"Probably."

I looked at jar again.

Black seed things.

Dry despite having apparently lived beneath drain.

No glow.

No obvious mana response I could feel without drawing.

I was not drawing.

No reason.

First-life memory offered several families.

Drain mites egg cases.

Silt pearls.

Drowned cinder beetle pupae.

Certain root pods that crawled toward moisture.

None useful without local context.

Good.

I said, "We're taking it to drain keeper."

Naro brightened.

"Can I come?"

"You are the witness."

"What's witness?"

Yesterday followed me home.

"You found it. You show exact place."

"Guide."

"You are guide."

He liked that better.

Mevi said, "I am coming because his exact place changes when he talks."

Also good.

I stood.

Harness stayed table.

No need.

Shoulder bag stayed mostly normal.

Oval was in it.

I considered removing oval because weird jar plus unexplained retreat object created unnecessary temptation.

Then decided no.

Oval was regular carry now.

Not using it was different from pretending I never carried it.

"Cala, jar stays closed?"

She looked at me like question insulted house.

"Yes."

"Good."

Naro reached.

I reached first.

"I'll carry."

"I carried here."

"And shook it."

"Before Mevi said."

"History remains."

I wrapped jar tighter in dishcloth and put it in shallow market basket Cala handed me.

Not bag.

If glass cracked, I wanted problem visible and away from papers.

Lyssa said, "If it hatches in house while you're gone, I'm charging Naro rent."

Naro looked at jar.

"Can they hatch?"

"She doesn't know."

Lyssa said, "Correct."

"Then why say?"

"Because now you'll stop shaking it forever."

Mevi nodded slowly.

"Useful."

Lyssa had found pedagogy.

We left.

Naro immediately walked ahead.

Mevi pulled him back by shirt.

"Guide means show, not vanish."

"I know way."

"Greg doesn't."

"He lives here."

"He doesn't live in drain."

I let them continue.

Street was warm after morning rain.

Gutters still carried thin water.

Yesterday's Pell mural remained at Fish Row when we crossed far end.

People had added more chalk.

I did not stop.

Good.

Different day.

At old red wall, Naro pointed toward iron grate at curb.

"There."

Mevi said, "No."

"Yes."

"You found it farther down."

"Same drain."

"Exact place."

Naro sighed like burdened surveyor.

We continued thirty paces.

Covered channel ran beneath lane.

Stone lip widened where maintenance hatch sat between wall and narrow public wash trough.

One rusted iron bar crossed opening.

Mud collected below.

Naro pointed.

"There."

Mevi said nothing.

Confirmation.

I crouched as much as left leg/crutches allowed.

Did not put hand under anything.

The drain mouth had fresh black smears along inner stone.

Not tar.

Maybe plant residue.

Three tiny black shapes clung above waterline.

Same size as jar contents.

One moved.

Definitely moved.

"Do not touch," I said.

Naro said, "I know now."

Growth.

A woman washing bucket at trough looked over.

"Those again?"

There.

Local knowledge.

I turned.

"You know them?"

"No."

Almost.

"But again?"

"Saw black little things yesterday. Drain crew scraped them."

"Which crew?"

She pointed downhill.

"Blue hooks."

Useful.

Carrow municipal uniforms continued being color plus object.

"Where do blue hooks stage?"

"Lower sluice by bell bridge."

"Thank you."

Naro whispered, "I knew that."

Mevi said, "No you didn't."

"I knew bridge."

"Everyone knows bridge."

We walked downhill.

Jar clicked once in basket.

I stopped.

Naro stopped too.

"Did you shake?" he asked.

"No."

"Then they shake themselves."

Possible.

Not conclusion.

I resumed.

Lower sluice sat where covered neighborhood drains opened into wider stone run before riverward channel.

Two workers in blue canvas vests had long iron hooks, exactly as advertised.

One stood knee-deep on dry side shelf scraping root mat from grate.

Other sat on wall eating something wrapped in leaf.

I approached seated one.

"You drain keeper?"

She swallowed.

"No."

Of course.

"Drain crew?"

"Yes."

"We found these under west drain near old red wall. Someone there said your crew scraped similar yesterday."

I set basket on wall, unwrapped enough glass to show contents without opening.

Her expression changed immediately.

Good.

Recognition.

"Don't open that."

"Wasn't planning to."

"Where exactly?"

Naro stepped forward.

Guide time.

"Under maintenance mouth by red wall after wash trough, not first grate, second opening where one iron bar is rusty and mud sits under left side."

Mevi looked at him.

He looked pleased.

Accurate when role mattered.

Worker stood.

"How many?"

"Seven," I said.

Naro said, "Eight before."

I looked at him.

"What?"

"I think eight."

Mevi closed eyes.

"You said seven."

"I said maybe."

"You did not."

I looked at jar.

Seven visible.

Could one be behind another.

Could count have been wrong.

Could one have changed.

No useful drama yet.

"Current visible seven," I said. "Original count uncertain."

Worker nodded.

Professional woman after my own heart.

"Did he open jar?"

"No," Mevi said.

"Shake it?"

Naro looked at ground.

"Once."

"How hard?"

"Not hard."

Mevi said, "Hard enough to hear."

Worker looked at Naro.

"Any dust come off?"

"No."

"Any smell?"

"Drain."

"From jar."

"I didn't smell jar."

"Good."

She called to partner.

"Sella!"

I knew that name.

The woman on dry shelf turned.

Of course.

Hanging Road Sella.

Operational lead who had once told people exactly where to put feet while road tried to become architecture.

She climbed out using side steps, saw me, then jar.

"Greg."

"Sella."

"Why do you have sluice seeds?"

There.

Name.

"Naro found them."

Sella looked at Naro.

"Where?"

He repeated exact location, slightly faster.

She listened.

"Jar already there?"

"Yes."

"Closed?"

"Yes."

"You open?"

"No."

"Shake?"

He hesitated.

"Once."

"Don't."

"I know now."

Sella accepted that.

She took jar only after putting on thick gray work gloves.

Not magical-looking.

Just gloves.

Held it toward light.

"Seven."

Other worker said, "He thinks eight before."

Sella looked at Naro.

"Did you count eight or think eight?"

Naro frowned.

Long pause.

"I saw... a lot. Then counted after Mevi yelled. Seven. Maybe I thought eight because one moved."

Good.

Sella nodded.

"Seven established."

No missing monster.

Thank you.

"What are sluice seeds?" Mevi asked.

Sella turned jar.

"Not seeds."

Naturally.

"Then why call them seeds?" Naro asked.

"Because they look like seeds."

"That's bad naming."

Sella looked at me.

"He's yours?"

"No."

Mevi said, "He's nobody's right now."

Naro objected.

"Mama's."

"Mama is at work."

"Still hers."

Sella handed jar to partner, who placed it inside lidded metal bucket.

"They're crawler cases," Sella said. "Small channel cleaners. City releases them in upper runs when silt mats get bad. They follow wet mineral line and eat soft buildup."

That sounded useful.

"Why in jar?"

"Because these were pulled."

"Why?"

"Wrong run maybe. Or spent. Or somebody collected them."

"Dangerous?"

"Not like that."

I waited.

Sella noticed.

"Don't eat them. Don't crush them in hand. Don't put live cases in household drain. They can chew soft lime and old seal paste if trapped where they shouldn't be."

Naro looked fascinated.

Mevi looked horrified.

"He was going to keep them."

"I was not."

"You said maybe."

"Maybe isn't going to."

Sella said, "Don't keep them."

"Fine."

Good.

I looked toward upper channel.

"You scraped similar yesterday?"

Other worker nodded.

"Nine cases. Different mouth."

Sella's face changed slightly.

Not alarm.

Work.

"Which mouth?"

"Red wall first grate."

Naro said, "See, same drain."

Mevi said, "Not exact place."

Sella ignored them.

"Any release scheduled upper west?"

Worker shook head.

"Not this week."

There.

Actual question.

Sella looked at jar again.

"These markings are old batch."

I saw no markings.

Of course.

"Where?"

She pointed through glass.

One black case had tiny pale notch near tip.

Then another.

"Cut mark?"

"Kiln stamp. Old north sluice batch."

"How old?"

"Could be months."

"Could they survive?"

"Cases can. Active crawler depends."

"You said live cases can chew seal paste."

"If active."

Good distinction.

"Are these active?"

"One moved."

Naro said, "They all move."

Sella looked at him.

"You saw all seven?"

"No."

"Then one moved."

Naro considered this injustice.

I liked Sella.

She turned to partner.

"Take bucket to lower office. Mark found west upper, second mouth and first yesterday. Ask Dren if old north batch was recovered after spring flush."

Partner nodded.

No conspiracy.

No city panic.

Administrative memory.

Exactly right.

Sella looked at us.

"Show me mouth."

Naro brightened again.

"I'm guide."

"Good. Guide."

He almost ran.

Mevi caught shirt.

Sella said, "Walking guide."

Naro walked.

We went back uphill with Sella carrying hook and a small chalk board clipped to belt.

I could have left.

Jar delivered.

Problem transferred to correct specialist.

But Naro and Mevi were still involved, and Sella had asked show mouth.

I stayed.

Not because expert.

Because adult-shaped enough for current route.

At red wall, Sella crouched beside maintenance opening.

No gloves needed because she did not touch cases.

Three black shapes still clung above waterline.

She counted.

"Three."

Then scraped tiny pale residue from stone with hook tip.

Smelled it.

I did not copy.

"Old mineral feed," she said.

"Meaning?"

"This run used to receive kiln rinse before reroute. Crawlers follow mineral line."

"So old batch could have stayed dormant somewhere and followed wet line after rain?"

Sella looked at me.

"Could."

Not yes.

Good.

"Or someone moved them."

"Could."

"Or release record wrong."

"Could."

"You hate conclusions too."

"Conclusions make paperwork."

Different motive than Hessa.

Same result.

She marked board:

WEST UPPER / OLD RED

3 VISIBLE LIVE-RESPONSE CASES

OLD NORTH MARK POSSIBLE

CHECK SPRING RECOVERY / KILN-RINSE LEGACY LINE

Then she looked at Naro.

"Where was jar?"

He pointed under lip.

"There. Behind mud ridge."

"Show without hand."

He used stick.

Good.

Sella scraped mud gently with hook.

Glass ring appeared.

Not another jar.

A broken jar base.

Same thick glass maybe.

Could be unrelated.

Sella exposed only enough to see.

"Do not touch," she said.

Naro put hands behind back dramatically.

Mevi said, "You don't have to perform."

"I'm showing."

Sella studied glass.

"Could be container broke and somebody put survivors in second jar."

"Who?" Naro asked.

"Don't know."

"Why?"

"Don't know."

"When?"

"Don't know."

"Are you drain keeper?"

"No."

I laughed.

Sella looked at me.

"Helpful."

"Sorry."

Not very.

She used hook to lift broken glass into small scrap pan from belt pouch.

Then chalked edge of mouth with blue X.

"Crew will screen this section before late water release."

"Should people avoid drain?" Mevi asked.

"People should always avoid putting hands under drains."

She looked at Naro.

"New rule."

Naro said, "What if coin?"

"Get adult."

"What if adult can't reach?"

"Get hook."

"What if no hook?"

"Lose coin."

He looked appalled.

"Depends how much."

Sella almost smiled.

"No."

"Copper?"

"No."

"Silver?"

"Find drain crew."

"So depends."

Sella stared at him.

Mevi covered face.

I said, "He got you."

"No," Sella said.

"Technically."

"Greg."

Apparently my name had become disciplinary tool across city.

We stepped away while she checked first grate.

Naro watched closely.

Not touching.

Good.

Mevi sat on wash-trough edge.

"Mama is going to kill him."

"Probably not."

"She said if he put hand in drain again she'd nail his sleeves to table."

"Probably also not."

"She might."

Naro said, "Loose nails."

Mevi pointed at him.

"See?"

I did.

Sella returned.

"Two more at first grate."

Five visible across two mouths today.

Plus seven jar.

Nine scraped yesterday.

Could be old batch surfacing after rain.

Could be local line residue.

Could be human movement.

Sella did not decide.

Neither did I.

"Anything we need do?" I asked.

"You? No."

Wonderful words.

"Children? Stay out of drains."

"Already policy," Mevi said.

"Enforcement issue," I said.

Naro glared.

Sella unclipped small square blue card from belt pouch and handed it to Mevi.

"If you see more above grate, tell blue-hook crew. Don't collect. Card shows lower office mark."

Mevi took it.

Not me.

Good.

"Thank you."

Naro said, "Can I have card?"

"No," Mevi and Sella said together.

Naro looked at me.

"No."

Betrayal complete.

Sella left to mark next channel.

We walked home.

Naro was quieter for nearly one block.

Then:

"Crawler cases are kind of animals?"

"Ask Sella next time."

"You don't know?"

"No."

"But you know magic."

"Some."

"And monsters."

"Some."

"And weird things."

"Some."

He frowned.

"You know less than I thought."

There.

Progress.

"Excellent."

"That's not excellent."

"It is for you."

Mevi laughed.

Naro did not.

At our door he stopped.

"I did good showing place."

"Yes."

No joke.

He had.

"And telling shake?"

"Yes."

"Even though bad?"

"Especially because bad."

He nodded.

That mattered more.

Mevi said, "I'll tell Mama he told truth."

Naro looked relieved.

Then suspicious.

"Tell her after I explain."

"No."

"Mevi."

"No."

They went two doors east arguing about order of confession.

I went inside.

Lyssa had moved from three finished wrap loops to eight.

Stack complete.

"Done?" I asked.

"With rework. Not returned."

Important.

"How was jar?"

"Sluice crawler cases."

She stopped.

"In house?"

"Closed jar."

"Greg."

"For less than ten minutes."

"Greg."

"Cala was here."

Cala said, "Do not involve me."

Abandoned.

I explained.

Old batch marks.

Drain crew had seen others yesterday.

Five more visible today.

Possible old kiln-rinse mineral line.

Possible dormant old batch.

Possible moved cases.

No conclusion.

Blue-hook crew screening section before late water release.

Lyssa listened.

Then said, "So Naro put hand under drain."

"Yes."

She returned to folding wraps.

"That's the important part."

Household risk assessment.

Tavi entered.

"What jar?"

No.

"Nothing."

"You said jar."

"I did not."

Lyssa said, "He did."

Traitor.

Tavi looked at me.

"What jar?"

"Drain creatures."

His eyes widened.

"Where?"

"Gone."

"Can we get some?"

"No."

"Why?"

"They eat drains."

"Cool."

"Wrong lesson."

"Can they eat spoon?"

Everything returned to spoon eventually.

"I don't know."

"We could test."

"No."

"Science."

"Still no."

He left disappointed in institutional research.

I sat at table.

Porter harness still lay where I had left it.

Oil had soaked into dry edge.

I wiped excess.

No heroic use.

No artifact.

Just maintenance after morning got interrupted by jar.

I thought about Naro saying I knew less than he thought.

Good.

People had started bringing me strange things.

That was dangerous in different way than strange things themselves.

Reputation could become claim before skill did.

I did know more than Naro about some things.

Enough to keep jar closed.

Enough to ask exact place.

Enough to recognize when local specialist mattered more than old-life category memory.

Not enough to identify sluice cases by sight.

That was fine.

Guide did not always mean person in front.

Sometimes it meant knowing which door to walk someone toward.

I looked at notebook upstairs in my mind.

No need to write crawler theory.

Not mine.

But one line maybe.

Later I did.

Not about crawlers.

I wrote:

IF PEOPLE BRING UNKNOWN OBJECTS: CLOSED / PLACE / WHO KNOWS LOCALLY

Then stared.

Too much like system.

Useful system, though.

I added:

DO NOT BECOME WEIRD-THING OFFICE

Better.

Nine copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Magic unchanged.

No new object.

No secret jar.

No missing eighth crawler waiting in my bag.

Thank gods.

Guide.

Mostly toward someone else.

# CHAPTER 373

## THE RIGGER

The wardrobe tried to kill Lyssa's contract.

Not Lyssa.

Important distinction.

The wardrobe had standards.

It chose cloth.

I was downstairs when something hit floor hard enough to make Tavi shout, "I didn't do it."

This was not reassuring.

Cala shouted, "Upstairs."

Also not reassuring.

I took stairs faster than dignity preferred.

Left crutch.

Right.

Good leg.

Repeat.

By time I reached landing, Lyssa was standing in bedroom doorway with both hands on head.

Not injured.

Good.

Behind her, old wardrobe leaned forward at angle furniture should not achieve voluntarily.

One front foot had punched through floorboard.

The whole thing now rested partly against edge of work table.

On that table sat second-lot cloth.

Of course.

"Don't touch it," I said.

Lyssa looked at me.

"I was planning to wrestle it."

"You look capable."

"Greg."

Right.

I looked.

Wardrobe tall.

Heavy.

Old dark wood.

Two doors.

One open because tilt had pulled latch loose.

Inside hung Cala's winter coat, Lyssa's charcoal coat, three household blankets, and one garment Tavi had apparently hidden because sleeve protruded from bottom shelf.

Front-left foot had gone through board near wall.

Front-right foot remained on floor.

Rear feet probably still loaded but I could not see.

Top leaned maybe handspan toward work table.

Table had caught door edge, not main body.

Bad.

If table shifted, wardrobe might go farther.

If wardrobe went farther, table and cloth went with it.

Maybe wall.

Maybe Lyssa if she stayed stupidly close.

"Back."

She backed.

Good.

Cala came up behind me.

One look.

"Floor."

"Yes."

"Wet?"

"Maybe old leak."

She looked at ceiling corner.

Faint stain.

Not new.

"That board has sounded soft," she said.

"How long?"

"Long enough I should have moved wardrobe."

No defensiveness.

Just fact.

"Nobody goes near it."

Tavi appeared at stairs.

"I can fit under."

"Especially you."

"I didn't say go under."

"Your face did."

He frowned.

Cala pointed downstairs.

"Kitchen."

"But."

"Kitchen."

He went.

Slowly enough to hear everything.

Lyssa looked at table.

"I have twelve cut apron fronts there."

"They are cloth."

"They are measured cloth."

"Still cloth."

"You don't understand."

I did not.

That was okay.

"Can you lose them?"

"I can redo them if material isn't damaged. If wardrobe crushes table and tears them, I need explain supplied-material loss."

There.

Actual consequence.

Not death.

Contract.

Time.

Trust.

House.

"Fine. We save cloth without becoming cloth."

"What?"

"Nothing."

I looked at wardrobe again.

First-life experience supplied many ways to move heavy furniture.

Most assumed two legs, two hands, wedges, rope, helpers, or willingness to damage furniture.

Current life supplied one good leg, two crutches, household rope, some neighbors, and locked chest full of bad ideas.

One of those ideas was called holdfast.

I stared at floor.

No.

Then wardrobe.

Then floor.

Maybe.

Bad word.

I hated maybe around load.

I looked toward the locked chest.

Cala saw it.

"No."

Lyssa looked between us.

"Absolutely not."

"Holdfast broad family includes anchoring, rigging, restraint, load control."

"Broad family," Lyssa repeated.

"Yes."

"Exact function unknown."

"Yes."

"Safe load unknown."

"Yes."

"Safe surface unknown."

"Yes."

"Release behavior unknown."

"Yes."

"No."

Strong argument.

I looked at table.

Wardrobe creaked.

Everyone stopped.

Tiny movement.

Maybe settling.

Maybe table flex.

Maybe floor.

No more debate.

"We need brace first," I said. "Normal brace."

Cala nodded.

"Jessa."

Of course.

Jessa knew rigging.

Not artifact.

Load.

Cala went downstairs.

Lyssa stayed doorway.

I stayed with her.

We did not touch anything.

That was action too.

Three minutes later Jessa arrived carrying two wood wedges, short pry bar, coil rope, and expression of person offended furniture had started without her.

"Show."

I showed.

She crouched outside fall line.

Looked under wardrobe from side.

"Front-left through. Rear-left sinking."

Worse.

"Table holding door only," she said.

"Yes."

"Don't trust it."

"Wasn't."

She looked at ceiling beam.

Then wall studs by tapping.

Then floor.

"Need keep it from coming farther before unload."

"Rope to beam?"

"Beam angle bad. Pulls top sideways."

"Wall?"

"Stud maybe. Not enough without opening plaster."

"Neighbors?"

"Bodies don't fix anchor."

Good.

She looked at me.

"What do you have?"

Dangerous question.

"Normal or weird?"

"Useful."

Lyssa said, "He has a holdfast he is not allowed to use."

Jessa looked at her.

Then me.

"Why not?"

"Unknown exact function, fixture, load, safe direction, release behavior, condition."

Jessa stared.

"Then that's not useful."

I liked her less briefly.

"It responded on Mara's bench to small ring movement. Edge line showed gross response near-right. No visible base shift."

"Meaning?"

"Meaning exactly that."

"Not anchor."

"Not established."

"Then no."

Two votes.

Wardrobe creaked again.

Jessa swore.

Longer than Greg-style.

Good for her.

She went to landing window and shouted down.

"Olin!"

Apparently Olin was outside.

Carrow social density remained unreasonable.

A voice answered, "What?"

"Bring two scaffold feet and long plank."

"Why?"

"Furniture."

Pause.

"Of course."

Five minutes later Olin came up with two iron screw feet and plank over shoulder.

I had not seen him in while.

He looked at wardrobe.

Then me.

"You buy this?"

"No."

"Good."

"Thank you."

Jessa explained quickly.

Olin talked more than Jessa.

"If we bridge sound boards here and here, feet can take plank under top rail, but we're lifting from front and that can walk rear feet if left side already soft, so I'd rather not lift, just catch. We catch, unload doors and shelves, then decide if move whole thing or cut floor around foot."

There.

Longer speech.

Actual person.

Jessa nodded.

"Catch only."

They set scaffold feet outside bad boards.

One near doorway.

One closer wall where floor sounded solid.

Plank across padded tops.

Not touching wardrobe yet.

Olin adjusted screws until plank sat finger below upper front rail.

"If it drops, catches."

"If plank kicks?" I asked.

"Feet are wide. Still possible. Nobody in front."

Good.

"Now unload?"

Jessa shook head.

"Door movement can shift it. Rope first."

She threaded rope around upper body from side using long hook Olin had brought.

No one entered fall line.

Rope ends came back to doorway.

But anchor remained problem.

Stair post?

Wrong angle.

Window frame?

No.

Hall beam farther back?

Maybe.

Jessa measured with eyes.

"Could run to stair post through door, but line crosses where we need work."

Olin said, "And post is old."

Cala said, "Post is older than me."

"That's not measurement," I said.

"It's enough."

Fair.

I looked at holdfast problem again.

Not as solution.

As object.

Circular base.

Ring.

Broad family.

Bench edge response after small ring turn.

No certified fixture.

No safe load.

No.

Still no.

Then I remembered something more important than artifact.

Mara.

Not object.

Person.

"How fast can we get Mara?"

Jessa looked at me.

"Registry Mara?"

"Yes."

"Why?"

"She has seen holdfast and knows what she didn't establish."

Olin said, "You want bring assessor to wardrobe?"

"I want ask whether there's any bounded use she would permit or whether answer remains no."

Lyssa said, "Better."

Jessa considered.

"Catch buys time."

"How much?"

"Until it doesn't."

Excellent.

Cala sent Tavi.

I objected.

"He's fastest," she said.

True.

She gave exact message:

"Mara Quill. South Registry Row. Tell her Greg has closed-housing holdfast she observed, possible emergency restraint question, no activation yet, furniture load, house floor failure. Ask if she can come or send no."

Tavi repeated it badly.

Cala made him repeat again.

Third time correct.

He ran.

We waited.

Not idle.

Jessa and Olin adjusted catch.

Cala cleared hallway.

Lyssa moved loose cloth stacks that were reachable without entering wardrobe fall line.

Not twelve apron fronts under table edge.

Those stayed.

I went downstairs to locked chest.

Not to use holdfast.

To have it ready if Mara said yes.

I unlocked cedar chest.

Three Reeds upright.

Rest stone boxed.

Holdfast wrapped.

Oval not here.

I lifted holdfast basket carefully.

Heavy enough to be annoying with crutches.

Cala met me halfway and took basket without discussion.

Good.

We placed it in hallway well outside room.

Wrapped.

Ring sideways.

No activation.

Jessa looked at it.

"That?"

"That."

"Looks like door fitting."

"Yes."

"I hate artifacts."

"You haven't met rest stone."

"I don't want to."

Healthy.

Mara arrived twelve minutes later.

Tavi behind her, proud and out of breath.

She carried work satchel.

Looked at wardrobe.

Looked at holdfast basket.

Looked at me.

"No."

Lyssa exhaled.

I said, "You haven't heard proposal."

"You don't have proposal. You have falling wardrobe and object with unknown safe load."

"Correct."

"No."

Jessa said, "Good."

I was surrounded by cowards.

Alive cowards.

Annoying category.

Mara crouched near floor, not wardrobe.

"What do you need?"

Jessa explained catch and rope issue.

Mara looked at holdfast again.

"If you want use as anchor, no."

"What if not anchor?"

She looked at me.

"Say actual thought."

I did.

"Bench response crossed near-right edge after ring movement. No base shift. We don't know whether force went into bench, laterally, internally, or something else. Could we use it only as unloaded indicator on a separate board to see whether wardrobe movement produces response?"

Silence.

Mara frowned.

"How would wardrobe movement couple to it?"

"Rope without tension. Slack line from wardrobe rope to holdfast ring. If wardrobe moves enough to take slack, line begins touching ring. We do not rely on it to hold. Catch remains actual safety."

Jessa's face changed.

Not approval.

Interest.

"Alarm," she said.

"Maybe."

Mara immediately said, "No maybe. It has never been established as alarm."

"Contact indicator experiment under real need?"

"Also no."

Fair.

I looked at wardrobe.

"Then why did you come?"

"Because message said emergency restraint question and no activation yet. I came to stop you."

Lyssa laughed.

Not supportive.

Mara stood.

"Also because I know a better thing."

There.

She opened satchel.

Pulled out two flat iron clamps with toothed jaws and long screw handles.

Mundane.

Beautiful.

"Beam dogs," Jessa said.

"Temporary," Mara said. "Rated for brace line on sound timber if teeth seat fully. Not artifact."

Olin took one.

"These fit plank."

"Yes."

Mara pointed to hall ceiling beam visible where plaster repair had left narrow exposed strip.

"And that beam is newer than stair post."

Cala said, "Flood repair."

Mara nodded.

"I can inspect seat. I am not certifying house."

Of course.

Within ten minutes we had actual plan.

Catch plank remained beneath wardrobe upper rail.

Rope around wardrobe body ran backward through doorway to beam dog seated on exposed hall beam, with second dog as redirect so line stayed out of work path.

Not lifting.

Not pulling wardrobe upright.

Only restraining further forward travel while unload happened.

Jessa tied.

Olin checked.

Mara checked clamp seats.

I did not touch rope because two crutches and pride were not rigging qualification.

Lyssa said, "Can we save cloth now?"

Jessa said, "After coats."

Wardrobe contents first.

They used long hook to pull hanging garments one by one from side.

Cala's winter coat.

Lyssa's charcoal coat.

Blankets.

Tavi's hidden shirt.

He shouted from hallway, "That's mine."

Cala said, "We know."

"I was looking for it."

Nobody answered.

Weight reduced.

Wardrobe shifted tiny amount backward against rope.

Not forward.

Good.

Jessa watched line.

"Stable enough for table."

Olin and Mara held table edge from safe side while Lyssa reached only far enough to slide cloth bundle toward doorway with yardstick.

Twelve apron fronts came out.

Then sleeve pieces.

Then work card.

Lyssa grabbed card first after apron fronts.

Priorities.

"All?" I asked.

She counted.

Long count.

Then again.

"All."

Shoulders dropped.

There.

Contract survived furniture.

Now house.

With table cleared, Jessa wanted wardrobe emptied fully before moving.

Bottom shelf had linens and one box.

Hook.

Slide.

No people in front.

Then doors removed from hinge pins using long reach and Olin's pry bar.

Weight down again.

Finally four adults could control body.

Not me as primary lifter.

I hated that for approximately one second.

Then remembered I enjoyed having shoulders.

My role became line call from side where I could see left rear foot through gap.

"Rear-left sinking when front comes back," I said.

Jessa stopped.

They shifted plank under base.

"Again."

Small lift.

"Hold."

Wedge under rear-left.

"Again."

Wardrobe came upright enough for front-left foot to rise from hole.

Board around hole crumbled.

Not dramatic.

Rot.

Old damp wood.

Cala swore quietly.

Wardrobe moved onto two bridging planks laid across sound boards.

Then against opposite wall temporarily.

No artifact.

No magic.

No injury.

One ruined floorboard and probably neighboring boards.

Mara looked at holdfast still wrapped in hall.

"You see?"

"Yes."

"Say it."

"Mundane clamps are beautiful."

"Other thing."

"Unknown artifact was not needed."

"Other thing."

I sighed.

"Real need does not make unknown load safe."

"Good."

Jessa said, "Write that down."

"I hate all of you."

Olin said, "You can hate after we inspect floor. Because if one board went, joist edge might be wet, and if joist edge is wet this becomes carpenter problem, and if you put wardrobe back before that you're going to repeat whole morning except maybe next time table isn't there to catch it."

Long speech.

Useful.

We inspected without opening more than already broken area.

Joist visible at edge.

Dark stain.

Firm under screwdriver probe Mara permitted Olin to do because apparently everyone had jurisdiction today.

Likely old leak damage concentrated boards, not obvious joist failure.

Not certified.

Carpenter needed.

Cala knew one.

Of course.

She knew everyone required to keep house standing except person who should have moved wardrobe earlier.

She said this herself before I could.

"I know."

Then, "It sounded soft. I left it. That's mine."

No melodrama.

Just responsibility.

Lyssa touched her shoulder once.

Cala leaned into it for half second.

Then started moving bedding.

Mara packed beam dogs.

I looked at them.

"How much?"

She looked at me.

"No."

"I asked price."

"They're mine."

"Where buy?"

"Tool Row. Common rigging stock."

"How much?"

"Depends jaw width and rating."

"Those?"

"Four copper each new. Two to three used if teeth good."

Interesting.

Not artifact.

Still interesting.

Jessa said, "You don't need beam dogs."

"Today I did."

"Today Mara had them."

"That's different."

"Exactly."

Damn.

I looked at holdfast.

Two copper purchase.

Two copper bench observation.

Still no useful function.

Meanwhile eight copper pair of boring clamps had just saved us from inventing risk.

Money was rude.

Mara saw calculation.

"Do not sell holdfast because clamps worked."

I blinked.

"I wasn't."

"You have comparison face."

Apparently many faces.

"Why not?"

"Because you still don't know what it is. Unknown does not mean worthless either."

Good.

"So keep expensive ignorance."

"Store it safely until question becomes affordable."

That was almost philosophy.

She ruined it by saying, "And stop trying to make emergencies into test conditions."

"I did call you."

"Yes."

"Before activation."

"Yes."

"Growth."

She considered.

"Some."

I'll take it.

Tavi appeared with bread.

"Can wardrobe stay there?"

"Temporarily," Cala said.

"Can I sleep in hole?"

Everyone said no.

He ate bread.

"Can spoon fit?"

I pointed downstairs.

He left.

Jessa and Olin left after rope came down and wardrobe remained stable on bridge planks against opposite wall.

Mara left after making me carry holdfast back to chest without unwrapping it.

Technically Cala carried basket.

I accompanied.

Still counts emotionally.

By afternoon carpenter arrived.

Not a character.

A carpenter.

Blessed category.

He opened two neighboring boards, found old localized water damage from long-resolved window leak, and said repair would need several boards replaced and wall edge checked before wardrobe returned.

Cala handled house cost conversation.

Not my house ledger.

I did not insert myself.

Lyssa relocated second-lot work downstairs for now.

Twelve apron fronts survived.

Everything survived except floor and my argument for artifact use.

At table, Lyssa resumed sewing.

"You wanted to use it."

"Yes."

"Badly?"

"Moderately."

"Liar."

"Strongly."

She smiled.

"But you didn't."

"I called Mara."

"You called Mara because three women told you no."

"Olin wasn't here yet."

"Two women."

"Still growth."

"Some."

Mara had infected her.

I went upstairs later.

Room looked wrong without wardrobe in original corner.

Hole in floor covered by temporary board.

Work table gone downstairs.

Space larger.

Ugly.

Real.

I opened notebook.

Nine copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Magic unchanged.

No money movement.

Then:

HOLDFAST: NOT USED

REAL NEED DOES NOT ESTABLISH SAFE LOAD

MARA BEAM DOGS: TEMP BRACE / SOUND TIMBER / 4C NEW EACH / 2-3C USED IF TEETH GOOD

I stared at last line.

Did I need beam dogs?

No.

Would I like beam dogs?

Yes.

Different disease.

I added:

ACCESS CAN BE PERSON, NOT OWNERSHIP

That one annoyed me.

Because true.

Collection did not need contain every useful thing.

Sometimes knowing Mara had beam dogs mattered more than having eight copper tied up in clamps I might use twice a year.

Sometimes Jessa knowing rigging mattered more than me knowing first-life rigging.

Sometimes Cala knowing carpenter mattered more than all of us staring at rotten board.

Accumulation was not only objects.

People were harder to store.

Probably good.

I closed notebook.

Downstairs Lyssa called, "Greg?"

"What?"

"Can you move lamp?"

I went.

Not artifact lamp.

Normal lamp.

I moved it six inches left.

She said, "Too far."

Three inches right.

"There."

Rigger.

Apparently.

# CHAPTER 374

## THE HOST

The first problem with inviting people over was that we already lived there.

This meant house looked like house.

Tavi's shoes under chair.

Lyssa's second-lot cloth occupying half kitchen table because upstairs floor had declared independence.

My crutches against wall.

Cala's onions hanging by stove.

One temporary board upstairs covering wardrobe hole.

Normal.

Apparently unacceptable once guests existed.

"Move that," Lyssa said.

I looked at my crutches.

"I need those."

"Not those."

"Helpful."

She pointed at stack of paper beside my elbow.

"Your notes."

"Those are organized."

"They are three piles."

"Exactly."

"What are piles?"

I looked.

"Known. Unknown. Things I am pretending I'll return to."

"Third pile upstairs."

"Floor problem."

"Greg."

I moved papers.

We were having dinner.

With people.

Not accidental people who wandered through doorway carrying jars or rope.

Invited people.

This had been Lyssa's idea three days ago before wardrobe attacked.

Then wardrobe attacked.

Then she said dinner should still happen because if we canceled every time house became inconvenient, Cala would never let anyone inside again.

Cala said this was slander.

Then moved two cracked bowls out of sight.

Guests were Senna and Merren.

An odd pair only if you assumed people arrived in matched sets.

Senna had been nearby for South Cloth committee sketch work.

Merren had finished lower counter shift early and apparently knew Senna through a cousin's stall awning that Senna once painted badly on purpose.

I had questions.

Nobody answered them.

Lyssa invited both because she wanted "people who aren't work people."

Merren was absolutely work person.

Lyssa said not ours.

Technicality.

I had contributed bread.

Bought earlier with household food money Cala handed me, not Greg ledger.

Important because nine copper remained nine copper and I refused to accidentally become accountant for dinner.

I also contributed seating geometry.

Wardrobe repair had displaced one upstairs chair downstairs.

We now had five chairs and one stool.

Six people if Tavi counted.

He did.

Aggressively.

"I'm not stool," he said.

"Nobody said you were."

"You looked at me then stool."

"I was counting."

"Count yourself stool."

Fair.

I took stool.

Left leg appreciated less than philosophy did.

We adjusted with folded cushion.

Host sacrifice.

Senna arrived first carrying nothing.

I respected this immediately.

"I was told not to bring food," she said.

"Correct."

"So I didn't."

"Excellent guest."

She walked in, saw cloth stacks, temporary relocated work table, Tavi's shoes, my stool, and said, "This is better."

Lyssa narrowed eyes.

"Better than what?"

"Clean houses. They look frightened."

Then Senna stared at west wall for long enough to become suspicious.

"No."

She looked at me.

"I didn't say anything."

"You're composing."

"Wall needs something."

"Wall has wall."

"That's why."

I pointed kitchenward.

"Sit."

She did not.

She wandered.

Senna's speech got shorter when looking, longer when explaining why everyone else had bad eyes.

Useful distinction.

Merren arrived with a jar of pickled radish.

Naro had damaged jar reputation.

I looked at it.

Merren noticed.

"Food."

"Thank you."

"Normal food."

"Better."

"Should I be worried?"

"No."

Lyssa from kitchen said, "Yes."

Merren took shoes off by door without asking.

Tavi inspected radish jar.

"Does it move?"

"If shaken."

"Don't," I said.

Merren looked between us.

"I missed something."

"Drain creatures," Tavi said.

"Dinner," Cala said.

Strong redirect.

We sat.

Cala had made rice, fried greens, fish with ginger, and thin broth.

Not feast.

Good dinner.

Lyssa put her second-lot cloth into two baskets and slid them under side table before food came out.

She kept work card on shelf.

I noticed because Ch373 had trained me to fear furniture.

No one needed know that.

Senna sat opposite me.

Merren beside Cala.

Lyssa beside Tavi because Tavi beside Senna would result in interrogation.

This lasted forty seconds.

"Why did you draw Greg?" Tavi asked.

Senna ate fish.

"He sat still."

"He doesn't."

"He did once."

"Why?"

"I paid him nothing."

"He said you wanted one copper."

I looked at Tavi.

"Why do you know that?"

"You said."

Probably.

Household privacy remained theoretical.

Senna said, "I wanted one copper for portrait. He didn't buy it. Then I sold studies."

"Did Greg get money?"

"No."

Tavi looked at me, scandalized.

"Your face made money and you didn't get any?"

"That's how art works."

Senna pointed chopsticks at me.

"No."

"Sometimes."

"Also no."

Merren said, "At counter if your face makes money, usually somebody filled wrong form."

Cala laughed.

Good.

Merren had not been invited to perform procedure, but procedure escaped anyway.

Lyssa said, "No work talk for first bowl."

"That was face talk," Merren said.

"You said counter."

"Context."

"Work context."

Merren considered.

"Fine."

She ate.

Senna said, "South Cloth committee hates my second layout."

Lyssa looked at her.

"That's work."

"Art."

"Paid?"

"Yes."

"Work."

Senna sighed.

"Your rules are oppressive."

"First bowl."

We made it six more minutes.

Then Tavi asked Merren whether carts could lie.

Nobody understood.

He explained.

"If counter says cart has six things but cart has seven, cart knows?"

Merren set spoon down.

"No."

"Then who lies?"

"Usually paper."

"Paper can't know."

"Correct."

"Then person."

"Sometimes person is wrong. Sometimes mark copied wrong. Sometimes one item added after count. Sometimes one removed. Sometimes two people use different lot boundary. Sometimes receiving count is wrong. Sometimes dispatch count is wrong. Sometimes nobody lied and numbers still disagree."

Tavi stared.

Merren had overexplained him into silence.

I loved it.

"Again," I said.

Merren looked at me.

"What?"

"Nothing."

Greg remained not only person allowed dry response.

That thought arrived with dangerous familiarity.

I killed it before it became manuscript note in my head.

Normal people could be funny.

Revolutionary.

Lyssa pointed at my bowl.

"Eat."

I ate.

Second bowl released work embargo.

Immediately everyone refused to talk work.

Of course.

Senna asked Cala how long she had lived in house.

"Twenty-two years."

"Same furniture?"

Cala looked toward ceiling.

"Some less successfully than others."

Wardrobe had reputation now.

Senna asked about wall repairs, old window leak, previous tenant who painted kitchen shelf blue then apparently changed mind halfway.

Cala told story.

Longer than usual.

Not because Cala became talkative.

Because house was hers.

Previous tenant was her aunt's friend Neli, who believed blue paint kept flies away because somebody in River Quarter told her. She painted one shelf, ran out of blue, used green, then decided flies were colorblind.

"Were they?" Tavi asked.

"They remained flies."

"That's not answer."

"It's all I have."

Senna got up mid-story to inspect shelf.

There was blue under chipped green.

She looked delighted.

"Don't paint it," Cala said.

"I wasn't."

"You looked."

Apparently face-reading contagious.

Merren asked why I had one chair upstairs if floor bad.

"Chair is on sound side."

"You checked?"

"Carpenter did."

"Good."

"No work talk."

"This is house safety."

Lyssa said, "You're worse than me."

"I count things."

"We know."

Dinner moved.

Not plot.

People.

Senna eventually asked Lyssa about coat.

Charcoal coat hung by door now because wardrobe exile.

"You bought it," she said.

Lyssa touched collar from chair.

"Yes."

"Nine?"

"Nine."

I looked at her.

Not because price new.

Because exact purchase had happened off my page somehow.

No.

Life happened when I wasn't present.

Healthy.

"Worth it?" Senna asked.

Lyssa thought.

"Ask after winter."

"You like it."

"I like looking at it."

"That's worth something."

"Not nine copper by itself."

Senna shrugged.

"Depends face."

"Coat doesn't have face."

"Everything has face."

Merren said, "Carts don't."

Tavi immediately said, "They could."

We lost ten minutes to whether carts had faces.

I voted yes if front lamps counted as eyes.

Lyssa said that was stupid.

Senna said wheels were ears.

Tavi said wheels obviously feet.

Merren tried to establish definitions.

Cala left table to get more broth.

When she returned, debate had reached axles.

She stood holding pot.

"I regret guests."

Good dinner.

After food, Tavi wanted game.

I did not know game.

Apparently everyone else did.

"Three Claims," Lyssa said.

"No."

"Why?"

"I don't know it."

"That's why."

Suspicious.

Rules simple.

Each person says three claims about self.

Two true, one false.

Others guess false.

Terrible game.

People could simply lie.

That was mechanism.

I hated it.

Tavi went first.

"I fell in river. I ate six eggs once. I saw ghost."

"Ghost false," I said.

"No."

"River false," Cala said.

"Yes."

I looked at him.

"You saw ghost?"

"Maybe."

"Then claim not established."

Everyone booed me.

Actual booing.

Hostile house.

"Game accepts maybe," Lyssa said.

"Then game has no standards."

"Your turn."

No.

Senna went.

"I broke my left wrist falling off roof. I once painted magistrate's dog blue. I hate pears."

Merren said, "Dog."

Cala said, "Pears."

Lyssa said, "Roof."

I looked at Senna's left wrist.

No obvious deformity.

Meaning nothing.

"Pears."

Senna smiled.

"Dog."

Tavi shouted.

"You didn't paint dog?"

"I painted magistrate's goat blue."

"That's cheating," I said.

"No."

"Near-truth."

"Still false."

Game was corrupt.

Merren went.

She took too long.

"I have never slept outside city wall. I can whistle through two fingers. I was engaged for eleven days."

Silence.

Interesting.

Tavi said, "Whistle."

Merren whistled so loudly Cala nearly dropped cup.

Tavi screamed with joy.

Senna said, "Engaged."

Merren shook head.

Lyssa said, "Outside wall."

"False. I slept outside twice."

I looked at her.

"Eleven days?"

She looked back.

"Eleven."

"Why?"

"Because on day twelve I wasn't."

Dry.

Earned.

No explanation.

Good.

Tavi demanded story.

Merren said no.

He demanded again.

Still no.

Boundary survived.

Cala's turn:

"I have punched a priest. I have never been farther than Bell Ford. I can make six kinds of dumpling."

I knew dumplings likely true.

Bell Ford uncertain.

Priest extremely plausible.

"Bell Ford false," I said.

Everyone else chose priest.

Cala looked at me.

"Why would you think I punched priest?"

"You asked question like answer was obvious."

She stared.

"Priest false."

Damn.

"Bell Ford true?"

"Never farther."

That landed differently.

Not sad.

Just fact.

Twenty-two years in house.

Never farther than Bell Ford.

First life I had crossed kingdoms until maps became suggestions.

Second life I had already traveled more than Cala in some directions and less in every way that mattered.

I did not say.

Lyssa's turn.

She looked at me before speaking.

Bad.

"I once cut my own hair with kitchen knife. I hate dancing. I have slept on roof."

"Dancing false," I said immediately.

She smiled.

"Yes."

Too easy.

"Kitchen knife?" Senna asked.

"Fourteen."

"Why?"

"Because my mother said no."

"How bad?"

Lyssa held hand near ear.

"Here."

Senna winced.

"Roof?"

"Summer. Too hot inside."

I pictured it.

Lyssa fourteen with terrible hair.

Then roof.

Different ages maybe.

Not mine.

Still liked images.

My turn.

Everyone looked at me.

I had problem.

First-life truths were unavailable.

Not because impossible to say.

Because saying them as game would be insane.

I had killed things larger than houses.

I had crossed frozen sea on foot.

I had slept inside dead titan rib cage during storm.

Two truths and one lie would destroy dinner.

Second-life truths were narrower.

Good.

"I hate olives. I have never broken a crutch. I can sew a button."

Lyssa laughed before anyone guessed.

"Crutch."

"Why?"

"You broke one before I met you."

"How do you know?"

"Cala."

I looked at Cala.

"Household privacy."

"The whole street heard."

Fair.

Tavi said, "Olives false."

"No. I hate olives."

Senna said, "Button false."

"I can sew button."

Lyssa looked offended.

"Show me later."

"No."

"Then unverified."

Hessa infected everyone too.

Game continued.

Then Senna changed it.

"One claim. Something nobody here knows."

Merren said, "That's not game."

"Better."

"How guess?"

"You don't."

"Then what is mechanism?"

"Knowing person."

Merren looked physically pained.

I understood.

Lyssa said, "Fine."

Senna first.

"I can't draw horses from memory."

I stared.

"You've drawn people moving."

"People are easier. Horses have too many wrong places."

Tavi said, "Four legs."

"Exactly."

Merren said, "I sing when I'm alone."

No one laughed.

She looked relieved.

Cala said, "I wanted to leave Carrow once."

Quiet.

Not dramatic.

Senna asked, "Why didn't you?"

Cala shook head.

"One claim."

Boundary.

Accepted.

Lyssa said, "I keep first bad thing I make when learning something."

I looked toward cloth baskets.

"You still have first apron?"

"Not this contract. First things."

"Where?"

"One claim."

Fine.

Tavi said, "I know where."

Lyssa looked at him.

"No you don't."

"Maybe."

"You don't."

He smiled.

My turn again.

Something nobody here knew.

Dangerous category.

Could choose trivial.

I had once eaten whole lemon peel.

Probably.

Could choose current-life private.

I sometimes woke reaching for left foot.

Too intimate for game.

I sometimes forgot age when tired because first life sat behind eyes like second calendar.

Absolutely not.

I looked around table.

Senna waiting without impatience.

Merren trying not to look like she was counting pause.

Cala quiet.

Lyssa watching me more carefully than game deserved.

Tavi bouncing knee.

I said, "I like being invited places more than I like going."

Lyssa smiled first.

Not teasing.

"That makes sense."

Senna said, "That's terrible."

"Why?"

"Because then host has to invite you and also hope you don't come."

"Efficient."

Merren said, "No."

Tavi said, "I like going."

"We know."

"Nobody asked mine."

"Go."

He thought hard.

"Sometimes I pretend asleep so Cala carries me."

Cala stared.

Tavi realized error.

Too late.

"You what?"

"Game over."

He ran.

Cala stood.

"Tavi."

"Host protection," he shouted from stairs.

"There is no host protection."

"Greg!"

I looked at Cala.

"Apparently there is."

She pointed spoon at me.

"You carry him next time."

"Impossible."

"Then no protection."

Fair.

Dessert was sliced pears.

Senna ate three.

Confirmed.

Merren helped Cala wash bowls despite being told guest.

Lyssa and Senna looked at coat again.

Tavi returned once legal danger passed.

I sat on stool with cushion shifted wrong and did nothing useful.

This felt suspiciously good.

Then lights went strange.

Not out.

Strange.

Oil lamp flame remained normal.

But west side of room brightened in thin pale bands across wall.

Senna stopped talking.

Merren turned.

Cala looked toward window.

Outside, evening had darkened.

Bands moved slowly upward.

Like reflected water.

Except no water high enough.

Tavi said, "Pretty."

Correct.

Lyssa said, "Greg."

Less correct tone.

I stood.

No mana.

No artifact yet.

At window, pale bands came from street side, sweeping across upper walls of houses.

People outside were looking up.

No shouting.

No bells.

No red boards.

Not emergency yet.

Then I saw source.

Across lane, old glass sign above closed spice shop had begun throwing light.

Not generating.

Catching something.

A faint blue-white shimmer moved along clouds, and sign glass split it into narrow moving bars.

Senna came beside me.

"Sky ribbon."

"You know it?"

"Seen once."

"Dangerous?"

"I was eight. Everything dangerous."

Useful.

Merren called from table, "If it's sky ribbon, west lanes sometimes cover mirror signs."

I looked at her.

"Why?"

"Glare. Horses. Cart drivers."

There.

Not cosmic event.

Traffic problem.

Carrow.

Outside someone shouted, "Cover glass!"

Then another voice farther down.

"West signs!"

People moved.

Cala already had old sheet.

I looked at Three Reeds chest location upstairs.

No.

Then paused.

Known artifact.

Known real capability: directional reduction of stray light beyond faced direction while preserving main lit region, at least in controlled lamp and ordinary oil-lamp contexts. Rear plate warms. Safe duration unknown.

This was not lamp.

Different source.

Not rated.

Do not use as experiment.

But actual need existed outside.

Real need does not establish safe use.

Yesterday's lesson.

Damn.

Mundane sheet existed.

We used sheet.

Cala and I went outside.

Senna followed because of course.

Merren followed because everyone did.

Lyssa stayed with Tavi and house.

Across lane spice-shop sign sat above reach.

Owner absent.

Neighbor had ladder.

People were already covering reflective signs with cloth.

Not ours.

We helped hold ladder.

I did not climb.

Cala climbed two rungs, neighbor higher, sheet over glass.

Bands vanished from our wall.

Down lane another sign flashed harder.

A horse shied.

Handler controlled it.

No injury.

Merren pointed.

"That one."

We moved.

For twenty minutes dinner became neighborhood sign-covering.

Not heroic.

Useful.

Senna knew which painted glass would split light worst by looking at angle and backing.

Merren knew two shop owners and where spare awning cloth was stored.

Cala knew whose ladder could be borrowed.

I held bases, carried folded cloth on right shoulder, and kept Tavi from joining because he escaped house once.

Lyssa retrieved him by collar.

"Host protection," he told her.

"No."

Good.

Sky ribbon itself strengthened briefly.

Pale blue-white arc visible between roofs.

Beautiful.

People stopped for maybe thirty seconds.

Even workers covering signs.

Then somebody said, "Still have three west windows."

Work resumed.

I loved city slightly for that.

By time ribbon faded, lane looked ridiculous.

Sheets tied over signs.

Awning scraps over upper windows.

One shop used trousers.

Nobody admitted whose.

We returned home.

Food cold.

Dessert mostly gone because Tavi.

"I saved two pears," he said.

There were six people.

"Generous."

We reheated broth.

Senna looked energized.

"I want paint that."

"Sky?"

"No. Trousers."

Of course.

Merren said, "Please don't paint identifying trousers."

"Why?"

"Because someone will know."

"That's point."

"That's exactly problem."

Cala sat down and laughed harder than line deserved.

Maybe tired.

Maybe dinner.

Maybe trousers.

Mood mattered.

Lyssa leaned against my shoulder briefly while standing behind stool.

"Good dinner," she said.

"We left halfway."

"Still."

"House almost killed contract yesterday. Sky attacked signs today."

"Tomorrow nothing."

"Don't say that."

She knocked wood.

Senna heard.

"Tomorrow I'm drawing Greg's wall."

"No."

"See? Something."

Merren left first because early shift.

Senna left later because she forgot time.

At door she said, "Invite me again."

There it was.

I liked being invited places more than going.

Apparently hosting had reverse problem.

People could ask to return.

"Maybe."

"That's yes."

"No."

"Lyssa?"

Lyssa said, "Yes."

Authority compromised.

Senna left satisfied.

After door closed, house became house again.

Bowls.

Cloth baskets.

Shoes.

Temporary table.

My crutches.

No guests.

Still felt different.

Not cleaner.

More used.

I opened notebook later.

Nine copper.

Twenty-three Vale.

Magic unchanged.

No artifact use.

I almost wrote sky ribbon.

Didn't.

Merren or city records knew sky ribbon better.

I almost wrote sign-covering procedure.

Didn't.

Not everything needed become mine.

Instead:

DINNER: SENNA / MERREN

Then after moment:

AGAIN: PROBABLY

I stared.

Host.

Apparently.

# CHAPTER 375

## THE LAMPLIGHTER

Jori sent for my lamp.

Not me.

Important distinction.

The note arrived folded twice and carried by theatre runner I recognized from house-route nights.

GREG

IF THREE REEDS STILL IN YOUR POSSESSION AND CONDITION UNCHANGED:
BRING IT TO WEST HOUSE AT SECOND BELL.
RATED LAMP ASSEMBLY AVAILABLE.
NEED SIDE-SPILL CONTROL FOR MASK REHEARSAL.
PAID EQUIPMENT USE IF SUITABLE.
NO MODIFICATION WITHOUT OWNER CONSENT.

JORI

I read it three times.

Lyssa read over shoulder once.

"You're smiling."

"No."

"Your face."

Apparently faces remained public infrastructure.

"Rated lamp assembly," I said.

"I can read."

"Paid equipment use."

"Also readable."

"No modification without owner consent."

"Greg."

"What?"

"Go get your weird lamp thing."

Shade unit.

But yes.

I went upstairs.

Floor repair had reduced path to locked chest into careful route along wall.

Carpenter's temporary boards were sound for walking according to carpenter.

Wardrobe remained opposite wall.

I unlocked cedar chest.

Three Reeds stood upright where left.

Cool.

No visible change.

Family card above it.

THREE REEDS SHADE UNIT
KEEP VENTS CLEAR
DO NOT ENCLOSE ACTIVE CORE
PAIR ONLY WITH RATED LAMP ASSEMBLY

Partial line below:

REDUCES STRAY THROW BEYOND SET FACE

There.

Today somebody had rated lamp assembly.

Actual compatible context.

I lifted unit carefully.

Not heavy enough to be problem by itself.

Awkward with crutches.

Porter harness could carry narrow parcels, but Three Reeds was not going into side loop because I enjoyed owning it.

Cala found me considering transport.

"Basket."

"Yes."

"Two hands?"

I looked at crutches.

"Rude."

She brought shallow padded basket with lid cloth, not tied shut around vents because unit inactive anyway and no reason to compress it.

"Who carries?"

"I can manage basket on right forearm short distance."

"To theatre?"

"No."

"Then who?"

Tavi shouted from downstairs, "Me."

"No," three voices said.

Lyssa had work.

Cala had house.

I could hire cart for absurdly small object and destroy dignity.

Or ask.

Access can be person.

Annoying lesson remained.

Jessa was unavailable.

Olin had yard work.

Senna would carry it while painting basket mentally and probably forget destination.

Merren at shift.

Then Nessa's niece passed front door.

No.

I was not turning city into delivery network.

I solved it normally.

I carried basket myself in short stages.

Right forearm hooked under handle while right crutch worked slightly wider.

Stop every block.

Set basket on wall or bench.

Rest.

Continue.

Slow.

Fine.

Owning objects included carrying them.

By second bell I reached theatre with shoulders annoyed but functional.

Jori met me at side entrance.

He looked at basket first.

Good man.

"Condition?"

"Unchanged since last controlled use. No activation since neighbor oil-lamp use. Rear plate warmed quickly there. Safe duration still unknown."

"Any drops?"

"No."

"Water?"

"No."

"Unauthorized opening?"

"No."

"Good."

He took basket only after asking.

Better man.

West House was not main stage.

Smaller rehearsal room with shallow platform, black curtains, two side galleries, and ceiling track carrying four lamp assemblies.

One assembly had been lowered to chest height.

Brass cage.

White ceramic socket housing.

Directional hood.

Stamped plate.

Jori pointed.

"Three Reeds compatible family."

I leaned closer.

Plate read:

TR-6 HOUSE LAMP
REEDS FACE ACCESSORY RAIL
VENT CLEARANCE 3 FINGERS
SIDE PLATE LIMIT: WARM / NOT HOT

Not exact shade-unit model.

But family.

"You have accessory rail."

"Yes."

"Why need mine?"

Jori's mouth tightened.

"House shade cracked."

He showed me.

On table lay similar unit.

Not identical.

Newer brass corners.

One face plate split diagonally.

"Replacement?"

"Ordered. Five days. Rehearsal today."

"And mine?"

"Mara's note from your prior controlled observation says yours responded as directional spill reducer in separate placement. Nessa remembered you used it here once. I checked old Three Reeds accessory dimensions against TR-6 rail. Likely fit. Not guaranteed."

Good.

Not coincidence.

Accumulation.

"What are you rehearsing?"

"Mirror masks."

Of course.

After sky ribbon yesterday, glass had personal vendetta.

Jori pulled curtain.

On stage stood three performers wearing half masks with small polished silvered inserts around cheek and brow.

Not magical as far as I knew.

Probably theatrical.

One turned head under side lamp.

White glare shot into opposite gallery.

I blinked.

"Problem."

"Yes."

"Why not cloth hood?"

"Kills face light."

"Move lamp?"

"Kills blocking."

"Different angle?"

"Tried."

He said each like inventory.

"What exactly do you want from mine?"

"Mount on rated accessory rail. Face west gallery. Run lamp at rehearsal setting. If spill reduction behaves inside known family context and rear plate stays below hot limit, we use for two rehearsal blocks. You stay present. I pay two copper equipment use."

Two.

I looked at him.

"Two copper for afternoon?"

"Equipment use plus owner attendance."

"Damage?"

"Theatre-caused damage to your unit during approved mounting/use, theatre pays assessed repair or agreed replacement value if repair impossible. Existing fault not ours."

"Replacement value unknown."

"Then assessed repair first. If destroyed and value disputed, Mara or another mutually accepted assessor."

Good.

"Heat stop?"

"Our TR-6 plate limit. Warm allowed. Hot stop. Also stop for smell, sound, discoloration, vent obstruction, unstable mount, effect change, or owner request."

"Owner request without cause?"

"Yes."

"Paid if stopped early?"

"One copper minimum once mounted and lit. Second after first full rehearsal block."

Jori had done this before.

Maybe not this object.

Equipment.

"Deal."

He wrote it.

Of course.

Nessa witnessed.

Of course.

I signed.

Nine copper remained nine until work happened.

We mounted Three Reeds.

Not me alone.

Jori handled lamp rail.

I handled shade unit.

Rail fit was close.

Two sliding shoes on unit base lined with accessory channel after we removed nothing.

No modification.

One locking pin on theatre rail passed through existing slot in shade base.

That slot had been mystery before.

Now not full mystery.

Accessory mount slot.

At least in this family context.

Jori stopped before tightening.

"Your consent?"

I checked vents.

Three fingers clearance behind.

Face plate oriented toward west gallery.

No cloth nearby.

"Yes."

Pin seated.

Unit stable.

No activation yet.

Lamp lit low.

Normal warm white.

Three Reeds inactive.

Performer turned mirror mask.

Glare hit gallery.

Jori marked chalk line where brightest spill landed.

Then he looked at me.

"Activate."

I used known control.

Three Reeds woke without drama.

Main stage face remained lit.

West-gallery spill dropped.

Not vanished.

Dropped enough chalk line no longer brightest point.

Performer turned head.

Silvered mask still flashed, but flash narrowed and died before reaching same gallery depth.

Jori said, "Again."

Performer repeated blocking.

Same.

Nessa walked gallery.

"Front two seats still catch. Back line clean."

Jori adjusted face orientation one notch using rated rail.

"Consent?"

"Yes."

Again.

Front seat caught less.

Stage face remained readable.

There.

Real.

Useful.

Mine.

Not because hidden god power.

Because correct object in correct system doing declared-family job.

I grinned.

Nessa saw.

"Don't."

"What?"

"Whatever you're about to infer."

"I wasn't."

"You were."

Fair.

Rear plate warm after several minutes.

Not hot.

Jori checked with back of fingers near but not on hottest plate, then theatre's little wax heat tab stuck to adjacent rail.

Tab stayed gray.

"Block one," he said.

Rehearsal started.

My job became boring.

Excellent.

I sat gallery aisle where I could see unit and performers.

No mana.

No touching.

Watch vents.

Watch rear plate indicators.

Listen.

Smell.

Effect.

The play scene involved three masked officials arguing over inheritance while pretending not to recognize one another.

Theatre remained weird.

One actor spoke like every word had paid rent.

Another rushed whenever angry.

Third had terrible habit of pausing before lies, which director hated.

"Stop announcing it," Nessa called.

Actor said, "I'm not."

"You breathe like guilty horse."

"That's my character."

"Then character is bad liar."

"He is."

Nessa stared.

"Fine. Keep it."

Art.

Three Reeds kept doing job.

At twelve minutes rear plate warmer.

Still below hot.

At eighteen, wax tab edge softened slightly but remained gray.

Jori called pause anyway.

"Cool."

We deactivated.

Main lamp remained on low.

Three Reeds effect ended.

Glare returned farther into gallery during test turn.

Good comparison.

We let unit cool fully.

Jori handed me one copper.

Actual copper.

I put it away.

Ten.

First time Three Reeds directly earned money.

That mattered more than amount should.

Not because copper.

Because object had crossed category.

From interesting purchase.

To useful household thing.

To equipment someone else would pay to use.

Asset.

Dangerous word.

Accurate enough.

During cool period I inspected theatre's cracked shade.

Did not touch.

"How much replacement?"

Jori said, "Current house unit? Eleven copper plus fitting."

I looked at mine.

Bought five.

Not same model.

Not same condition.

Do not immediately become rich.

"Used older?"

"Depends. Six to ten if family known and plate sound. Less if untested. More if rare fit."

Interesting.

"Mine?"

"Not my job to value."

"Coward."

"Correct."

Nessa said, "He means theatre shouldn't set purchase value while renting it."

Procedural ethics.

Annoying.

Good.

"Would theatre buy mine?"

Jori looked at cracked house unit.

Then mine.

"Not today. Replacement already ordered."

No magical windfall.

Fine.

"Would you rent again?"

"If compatible need and condition remains acceptable."

Better.

Block two started after full cool.

This time director changed blocking.

Performer crossed closer to west edge.

Mirror insert angle changed.

Three Reeds still reduced spill, but one sharp flash escaped above face line when actor looked upward.

Jori stopped.

"Not enough for that mark."

He adjusted lamp hood, not shade.

Small downward tilt.

Test.

Main face still good.

Upward flash reduced.

Three Reeds handled side spill.

System.

Not single object solving everything.

Rehearsal continued.

At fourteen minutes rear plate warming similarly.

At seventeen wax edge softened.

Jori ended block at eighteen again.

No need push.

Deactivated.

Cool.

Second copper.

Eleven.

I held two new copper in palm briefly before purse.

Not fortune.

But yesterday nine.

Today eleven.

Vale twenty-three.

Object had paid back two of five purchase price in one afternoon.

Terrible accounting if I ignored storage, risk, time, transport, uncertainty.

Still satisfying.

Jori unmounted only after unit cool.

Pin out.

Shade slid free.

No scrape beyond existing wear.

No smell.

No discoloration.

Rear plate returned cool.

We inspected together.

Condition apparently unchanged by visual check.

"Receipt," he said.

Of course.

He wrote:

THREE REEDS SHADE UNIT
OWNER: GREG
TEMPORARY THEATRE USE
TR-6 HOUSE LAMP ACCESSORY RAIL
2 REHEARSAL BLOCKS
18 MINUTES ACTIVE EACH
FULL COOL BETWEEN
2 C PAID
NO DAMAGE OBSERVED AT RETURN

I read twice.

"Add face orientation changed one rail notch first block."

He did.

"Add second block lamp hood adjusted, not shade."

He did.

Nessa signed witness.

I folded receipt into shoulder bag.

That paper mattered.

Not universal certification.

But provenance.

Use history.

Compatible family evidence.

Market information.

My object was becoming legible.

Slowly.

Correctly.

Jori said, "One more thing."

I waited.

"Costume loft has older Three Reeds rail plate without core. Broken housing. If you want dimensions, you can look before you leave. Not take."

There.

Access.

I wanted.

"Yes."

Of course yes.

Loft smelled like dust, wool, old paint, and thirty years of people sweating into costumes.

Beautiful.

Broken rail plate sat in crate marked LAMP SCRAP / DO NOT DISCARD.

No core.

No active plate.

Just brass base and two sliding shoes.

Same shoe spacing as mine.

Stamped underside:

TR-4 / TR-5 / TR-6
FACE ACCESSORY
REV C

Mine underside had grime.

I had never cleaned aggressively because unknown artifact and intelligence.

Now with Jori present, inactive/cool, I used dry cloth on small non-vent base patch.

Stamp emerged.

TR-5F

Then partial:

REV B

I stopped.

Jori leaned.

"Older face unit."

"F means face?"

"Likely. Don't know manufacturer notation well enough."

Good answer.

Mine compatible with rail family at least through actual fit.

TR-5F.

Not nameless anymore.

Still not full mechanism.

Still not exact safe duration.

But model fragment.

I wrote it.

Jori let me measure shoe spacing against scrap with theatre ruler.

Matched.

No money.

No ownership change.

No secret compartment.

No destiny.

Just information.

On way out Nessa said, "You know you're going to become unbearable."

"Become?"

She considered.

"Fair."

I carried basket home in same stupid stages.

Except now purse had eleven copper.

Basket felt lighter because psychology is dishonest.

At home Lyssa was still sewing.

She looked up.

"Well?"

I put two copper on table.

Not giving.

Displaying.

"It worked."

Her face changed.

Actual smile.

"Really?"

"Rated family rail. Two eighteen-minute blocks. Full cool between. Directional side-spill reduction useful for mirror-mask rehearsal. No damage observed."

"Greg."

"What?"

"It worked."

I smiled.

"Yeah."

Tavi came running.

"Did dark lamp make money?"

"Shade unit."

"Did shade unit make money?"

"Two copper."

His eyes widened.

"Can I borrow it?"

"No."

"I can pay one leaf."

"No."

"Two leaves."

"Market rejected."

He left to acquire more capital.

Cala inspected receipt because household had learned paper mattered around my objects.

"Eighteen minutes."

"Twice."

"Not thirty-six straight."

"Correct."

"Good."

Lyssa said, "How much did you pay for it?"

"Five."

"So three more and free."

I made face.

She laughed.

"I know. Not free."

"Thank you."

"Still."

Still.

I put Three Reeds back upright in locked chest after fully cool.

Receipt stored separately with artifact papers.

Family card protected.

Then notebook.

CASH: 11 C

VALE: 23 C

THREE REEDS: 2 C THEATRE EQUIPMENT USE

TR-6 RAIL: FIT CONFIRMED

UNIT BASE STAMP: TR-5F / REV B

2 X 18 MIN ACTIVE

FULL COOL BETWEEN

REAR PLATE WARM / NOT HOT

NO DAMAGE OBSERVED

Then:

FIRST DIRECT INCOME FROM OWNED ARTIFACT

I stared at that.

Owned artifact.

Not auction fantasy.

Not thing behind glass.

Not object I couldn't afford.

Mine.

Useful.

Imperfect.

Potentially worth more now because known history existed.

Potentially worth less if hidden fault appeared later.

Real object.

Real market.

Real use.

I added one last line.

DON'T GET STUPID.

Then under it:

GOOD WIN THOUGH.

I closed notebook.

Lamplighter.

Close enough.

# CHAPTER 376

## THE PASSENGER

The problem with having eleven copper was that eleven copper could go places.

Nine copper mostly sat.

Nine looked at eight-copper object and said absolutely not unless object also came with house.

Eleven looked at eight and said maybe.

Maybe was expensive.

I discovered this while standing beside southbound coach board.

Not because I planned to buy artifact.

For once.

Sella had found me outside baker.

"You busy?"

"Depends."

"Wrong answer."

"Then no."

"Good."

She handed me folded transit chit.

I looked.

CARROW SOUTH GATE
BELL FORD
RETURN
ONE PASSENGER
TODAY

"Why?"

"Hanging Road inspection reopening. Need ordinary route bodies after maintenance wagons."

"I'm ordinary route body?"

"For this purpose."

"Rude."

"You used road before closure. You use crutches. You notice grade changes because grade changes annoy you."

Better.

"Paid?"

"No. Free return ride, noon meal, and you get to Bell Ford."

I stared at chit.

Bell Ford.

Cala had never gone farther.

Not relevant.

Immediately relevant.

"What do I do?"

"Ride. Walk marked passenger section. Tell inspector if anything feels different from normal road use. Don't diagnose. Don't touch maintenance gear. Don't become helpful beyond assignment."

"That last one written?"

"No. I added."

Sella knew me too well for person I did not see often.

"Why me?"

"They asked for mixed mobility. One cart driver. One porter. One older passenger. One person with cane. You with crutches. Two ordinary walkers."

"I'm diversity."

"Today."

I took chit.

"When?"

"Half bell."

"I need tell house."

"Do that."

Then she left.

No pitch.

No conspiracy.

Free trip.

I went home.

Lyssa was not there.

Second lot had moved to Marra for interim count apparently, according Cala.

Tavi was doing something with string that looked criminal.

Cala looked at chit.

"Bell Ford."

"Yes."

She read return line.

"Today."

"Yes."

"Go."

No hesitation.

I looked at her.

"You want anything?"

"From Bell Ford?"

"I don't know."

"Then no."

"You've never been farther."

"Still true."

"Want me to tell you what it looks like?"

Cala folded chit and handed back.

"You'll tell us anyway."

Also true.

Tavi appeared.

"Can I go?"

"No."

"Why?"

"Inspection passenger."

"I'm passenger."

"Not invited."

"Invite me."

"I cannot."

"Ask."

"No."

He thought.

"Bring rock."

"Why?"

"Bell Ford rock."

"Fine."

"Good one."

"Define."

"You'll know."

Terrible procurement standard.

I left Three Reeds locked.

Holdfast locked.

Rest stone locked.

Oval stayed in shoulder bag because oval normally stayed there, not because trip was experiment.

Notebook.

Charcoal.

Water.

Crutches.

Eleven copper.

I almost left most money home.

Then didn't.

Choice after price only existed if money traveled with me.

Dangerous thought.

South Gate coach yard smelled like horse, dust, boiled grain, and old leather.

Inspection group gathered beside low passenger wagon with roof but open sides.

Not Bell Ford coach proper.

Maintenance transport.

Sella stood with clipboard woman I didn't know.

"Greg," Sella said. "Inspector Veya Dorn."

Veya was maybe fifty, square shoulders, hair braided tight enough to enforce regulations.

"Crutch user. Left lower limb loss?"

"Yes."

"Any current pain?"

"No."

"Recent injury?"

"No."

"Can walk quarter mile with rests?"

"Yes."

"Can decline any section?"

"I assume."

"You can. Say so."

I liked her.

Others:

Rell, older woman with cane and enormous hat.

Pavin, porter with shoulders shaped by profession.

Doss, cart driver missing two front teeth.

Ema and Cor, ordinary walkers, married maybe, argued like it.

And Lute, maintenance runner assigned to group.

Veya gave instructions longer than necessary and exactly as long as she wanted.

"Road has passed structural maintenance checks for limited inspection traffic. This is passenger-use observation, not structural certification. You will remain within marked route. If you feel movement, unexpected grade, surface slip, rail looseness, vibration, sound change, visual misalignment, or anything else you would ordinarily react to as passenger, say it. Do not create tests. Do not jump, stamp, shake rail, crowd edge, or attempt to reproduce prior failure. We are not asking courage. We are asking ordinary use."

Rell raised hand.

"Can complain about seats?"

Veya looked at wagon.

"Please."

Rell nodded.

Professional relationship established.

We boarded.

I took outside bench where crutches could lie under knees without stabbing stranger.

Rell sat beside me.

"You young for two sticks."

"You old for one."

She barked laugh.

"Good."

No offense.

Useful.

Wagon rolled south.

Carrow changed slowly.

Not dramatic gate into wilderness.

Work yards thinned.

Walls lowered.

Roofs spread apart.

More storage plots.

Then fields.

Actual fields.

I had seen them before from approaches, but leaving city with no job destination inside city felt different.

Open distance made body remember first life in annoying flashes.

Roads through kingdoms.

Snow.

Horse I hated.

Horse that hated me back.

Three months sharing saddle because left boot split and no cobbler for six days.

Not this life.

This wagon had suspension bad enough to become current problem.

Rell said, "Seat terrible."

"Inspector requested complaint."

"I'm contributing."

Doss from front said, "Road's better than wagon."

Veya wrote something.

Maybe wagon.

Hanging Road appeared after long slope.

I knew it immediately.

Stone path carried across cut where ground dropped toward creek channel.

Last time it had lifted while occupied.

Not flying.

Not dramatic.

Enough.

Enough is plenty when standing on road.

Now red closure boards gone.

New pale stone patches marked three sections along edge.
