Maintenance braces visible beneath outer lip where road crossed deepest cut.

Veya stopped wagon before first marker.

We dismounted.

"Walk section one in normal manner. No grouping requirement beyond don't overtake on narrow section."

Normal manner.

Crutches.

Right foot.

Left residual limb stable in socket.

Ground.

Move.

Road felt road.

First patch slightly rougher.

Not bad.

Different.

I said so.

Lute marked location.

Pavin said, "More grip."

Doss said, "Cart wheel will sing there."

Veya asked, "Meaning?"

"Different aggregate. Harder top. Wheel noise changes. Not danger. Driver notices."

Good.

Nobody tried agree.

Rell hated one seam because cane tip clicked into shallow groove.

I tested nothing.

I simply reached same seam and my right crutch tip caught edge enough to interrupt rhythm.

Not stick.

Catch.

"There," I said.

Veya came closer but did not step into path.

"Describe."

"Right crutch tip catches shallow seam if placed near line. Didn't trap. Broke cadence."

Rell pointed cane.

"Mine too."

Lute crouched after we passed and marked chalk beside seam.

No revelation.

Maintenance issue.

Section two had rail replacement.

New rail higher by maybe finger.

Fine.

Road itself sloped subtly outward before correcting.

I noticed because crutches told shoulders.

"Outward grade here."

Veya asked how long.

I walked until it stopped.

"From that patch to third post."

Pavin said he felt it under loadless boots only after I said it.

Doss said cart would tell better.

Veya wrote.

Section three was where road had lifted before.

My body remembered before brain did.

Shoulders tightened.

I stopped.

Veya noticed.

"Pain?"

"No."

"Road sensation?"

"No. Memory."

She waited.

Good inspector.

I moved when ready.

No lift.

No tremor.

No strange sound.

Oval in bag did nothing I noticed.

I did not check it.

Ordinary use.

At far marker Veya asked each separately.

Doss: "Surface fine. Need loaded cart pass for seam noise."

Pavin: "Outer grade noticeable. Would matter with shoulder load."

Rell: "Fix cane groove."

Ema: "Rail feels too high."

Cor: "Rail is supposed to be high."

Ema: "I said feels."

Cor shut up.

Smart.

Me: "Same cane seam catches crutch tip. Outer grade from patch to third post shifts load into shoulders. Former lift section felt ordinary today."

Veya said, "Good."

Not praise.

Receipt of data.

We walked back only first two sections, then wagon carried us south.

Hanging Road inspection complete for our group.

I expected return.

Instead road continued.

Bell Ford sat another half hour away.

Free trip meant actual Bell Ford.

I had somehow forgotten.

Fields widened.

Low stone fences.

Irrigation channels.

One orchard with silver netting over half trees.

Not decorative.

Something moved under net like light avoiding leaves.

I looked.

Rell said, "Sun gnats."

"Those?"

"You don't know sun gnats?"

"Not local ones."

"They eat bloom shine."

That explained nothing.

She continued because silence offended her.

"Not flower. Shine on flower. If they take too much, fruit sets ugly. My sister had plum rows. Net keeps them high. Unless rain. Rain makes them stupid."

"Dangerous?"

"To plums."

Good fantasy ecology.

Normal problem.

Bell Ford announced itself by bells.

Obviously.

Not giant tower.

Small bronze bells hung from bridge rails in uneven clusters.

Wind moved some.

Water moved others through thin cords tied beneath bridge.

Sound layered.

Not music exactly.

Place had maybe one-fifth Carrow density.

Road widened into market square before river crossing.

Stone bridge broad enough two wagons.

Low houses.

Blue tile on public roofs.

More trees than Carrow center.

Smell of river and smoked meat.

Veya said, "Meal. One bell free movement within square and bridge approach. Return wagon leaves from same post. Miss it, chit does not cover later coach."

Rell immediately vanished toward dumpling stall.

I stood.

Bell Ford.

No assignment.

No one waiting.

One bell.

Passenger.

I liked it.

No, I liked having gone.

Different.

I ate inspection meal at public stall token Veya provided.

Stew, bread, pickled cabbage.

No personal spend.

Then walked market.

Slow.

Not shopping.

Looking.

That lasted seven minutes.

A table outside old ironmonger's had sign:

ROAD / FIELD / HOUSEHOLD ODDMENTS
NO POWERED TESTS IN SQUARE
ASK BEFORE HANDLING

Fuck.

I stopped.

Eleven copper had traveled.

Maybe.

Table held ordinary things first.

Good sign.

Two brass hinge sets.

A cracked survey lens.

Three old lamp collars.

A ceramic line weight.

Bundle of weatherproof tags.

Then object shaped like flattened black horseshoe with pale bead set at each end.

Not obviously magical.

Which meant nothing.

Card:

OLD FORD STEP KEEPER
PASSIVE
PAIR INCOMPLETE
CURRENT RESPONSE UNKNOWN
6 C

Six.

I had eleven.

Five after.

Reserve.

Actual reserve.

I hated progress.

Shopkeeper was woman maybe sixty with one eye cloudy and both hands black from metal dust.

"What did it do?"

She looked at object.

"Which story?"

"Documented one."

"Better question."

She reached under table, produced paper copy.

OLD FORD STEP KEEPER
WEST APPROACH SERVICE
PAIR DEVICE
PASSIVE EDGE COUNT / RETURN INDICATION
LEFT HALF REMOVED AFTER POST REBUILD
RIGHT HALF RETAINED
PAIRING MARK LOST

I read again.

"Edge count?"

"Old bridge crew used pairs to tell whether crossing tally returned even after fog or night closure. One each side, supposedly. Don't ask me exact mechanism. This half came from west approach shed clearance. Other half gone before my lot."

"Current response unknown."

"Because half device."

"Why six?"

"Brass and bead worth one. Provenance and intact passive housing worth rest. Somebody with mate or collector interest may pay."

Rational.

Annoying.

"Any response alone?"

"Sometimes bead clouds when carried across threshold."

I looked at her.

"Sometimes?"

"Seller claim. I saw once from shed to cart. Did not repeat cart to stall. Could be temperature, handling, nonsense. I don't price it as working."

Interesting.

Too interesting.

Oval in bag suddenly felt heavier.

Threshold-related object.

No.

Do not combine because nouns rhyme.

Retreat oval not universal threshold machine.

This had documented bridge pairing/count/return indication.

Different.

Maybe.

"Can I inspect inactive?"

"Hands clean?"

I showed.

She handed cloth first.

I wiped fingers.

Then object.

Cool metal.

No vibration.

Two pale beads dull.

Underside had three mounting holes and partial stamp:

BF-W / 2

One edge scarred where something once attached or rubbed.

Housing screws old but not mangled.

I did not open.

"Five," I said.

She laughed.

Not insulted.

"Six."

"Pair incomplete. Current response unknown."

"Card says that. Six."

"Seller claim didn't repeat."

"Also card says current unknown."

"You really want six."

"I really want six."

I set it down.

Five reserve if bought.

Five copper was not nothing.

Object had documented provenance but missing mate.

Potentially useless alone.

Could become research problem.

Could sit chest.

Could be six copper curiosity.

I thought of Mara:

Twenty does not mean buy twenty-copper thing. It means still have money after buying eight.

Eleven was not twenty.

But six left five.

Choice after price.

I looked around table.

"How long here?"

"Today and tomorrow unless sold."

Return wagon in less than bell.

Could walk away.

Come back tomorrow?

Travel cost unknown because today's chit free.

Could ask hold.

No.

Nonrefundable hold had already taught enough.

I asked, "Any reason price changes tomorrow?"

"If still here, no. If someone offers six, gone."

Clean.

I picked it up again.

Not magic hunger.

Information gap existed, but market knew gap.

Price reflected provenance, intact housing, collector/mate possibility.

No stupid seller.

Could I use it?

Unknown.

Could I learn from it?

Probably.

Would five copper reserve keep life flexible?

Yes, more than zero.

Would six copper prevent debt payment?

Debt not due immediately; review six weeks from Ch343, now some time passed but no new due date. Still obligation.

Could I earn again?

Maybe.

No guaranteed work.

I set it down.

Shopkeeper waited.

I walked away.

Three stalls.

Then stopped.

Damn it.

Not because fear.

Because I had eleven specifically so every interesting thing did not have to become no.

Reserve was not museum where money sat forever.

Optionality existed to choose.

Question was whether this was choice worth making.

I went back.

Shopkeeper did not smile.

Professional.

"Six."

"Six."

I paid.

Six copper.

Eleven to five.

She wrote receipt.

OLD FORD STEP KEEPER
RIGHT HALF / PAIR INCOMPLETE
BF-W / 2
CURRENT RESPONSE UNKNOWN
WEST APPROACH SHED CLEARANCE PROVENANCE PER LOT PAPER
6 C

No guarantee.

I asked for copy of lot paper.

One bit copying fee?

No.

She included rough copy because already made duplicate for sale.

Thank you, universe.

Bit continuity survived.

She wrapped object in oilcloth.

"Don't mount it to active bridge hardware."

"Wasn't planning."

"Everyone says that until they own bridge thing."

"I don't own bridge."

"Good start."

I put wrapped step keeper in shoulder bag separate from oval.

Separate pockets.

Not touching.

No experiment.

No combining.

I had bought artifact.

Maybe artifact.

Documented service object.

Incomplete.

Six copper.

I felt fantastic.

And sick.

Five copper.

Still five.

Not broke.

Not desperate.

Five.

This was new.

I found Tavi's rock near bridge approach.

Smooth gray stone with white stripe.

Free.

Best price of day.

On bridge, bells moved in river wind.

I walked halfway.

Not farther because free movement boundary included bridge approach and square, and I had no need test wording.

Cala had never been farther than Bell Ford.

I was at Bell Ford.

I did not know whether that counted as farther.

Probably not.

I looked south beyond bridge.

Road continued.

Of course.

First life roads always continued until something killed traveler or map.

Second life too.

Return wagon bell rang.

I went back.

Passenger.

For once that was enough.

Ride north, Rell noticed package.

"Bought trouble?"

"Maybe."

"How much?"

"Six."

She whistled.

"Then definitely trouble."

"Useful insight."

She showed me three dumpling packets.

"I spent two bits. Mine tastes better."

No conversion.

No comparison.

Just judgment.

At Hanging Road, wagon crossed under limited inspection permission.

We remained seated.

Different test group maybe cart load.

Doss listened to wheel over rough patch.

"There."

Veya heard.

"Different aggregate?"

"Yep. Not bad. Just sings."

At seam where cane/crutch caught, maintenance crew already had chalk and measuring wedge.

Good.

Our observation mattered before day ended.

That satisfied almost as much as purchase.

Almost.

Back Carrow near dusk.

Veya collected chits.

"Thank you. Passenger notes will be included before reopening decision."

"When decision?"

"Not yours."

I smiled.

"Fair."

Sella waited by gate.

"You bought something."

"Why does everyone know?"

"Package."

"Could be bread."

"You don't look guilty about bread."

I showed receipt, not object.

She read.

"Step keeper."

"Know it?"

"Know name. Old ford crews used paired tally hardware before current gate boards. Never worked with one."

Good.

"Six stupid?"

She handed receipt back.

"Ask someone who values old bridge junk."

"Helpful."

"You had six?"

"Had eleven."

"Now five."

"Math."

"Good. You can still eat."

"I could eat before."

"Then why ask me?"

Excellent question.

Home.

Tavi met me at door.

"Rock?"

I handed gray-white stripe stone.

He inspected.

"Good."

No explanation.

Procurement success.

Lyssa was home now.

She saw package.

Then my face.

"How much?"

"Hello."

"How much?"

"Six."

She closed eyes.

Not angry.

Calculating whether murder worth paperwork.

"You had eleven."

"Yes."

"So five."

"Everyone can math today."

"What is it?"

I put receipt on table.

Not object.

House rules improving.

Cala read over Lyssa shoulder.

Tavi tried climb chair.

I pointed.

He got down.

Lyssa read aloud slowly.

"Old Ford Step Keeper. Right half. Pair incomplete. Current response unknown."

She looked at me.

"You spent six copper on half a thing that may not work."

"Yes."

"Why?"

Good question.

I could say provenance.

Could say passive edge count / return indication.

Could say intact housing.

Could say information gap.

Could say I wanted it.

"Because I wanted it and five copper left was enough reserve for me to choose six."

Lyssa blinked.

That answer surprised both of us.

Cala said, "Better than pretending investment."

"It might be investment."

"There he is," Lyssa said.

I laughed.

Then explained documented function.

No testing tonight.

No pairing with oval.

No mounting.

We opened wrapping only enough for visual household inventory after hands clean and table cleared.

Tavi called it bridge horseshoe.

Better name than dark lamp.

I stored it in cedar chest on separate padded cloth, not touching other artifacts.

Receipt and lot-paper copy with artifact papers.

Chest getting crowded.

Real constraint.

Not today.

Notebook:

CASH: 5 C

VALE: 23 C

BELL FORD TRIP: INSPECTION CHIT / NO PAY / MEAL INCLUDED

HANGING ROAD PASSENGER OBSERVATION:
- CANE/CRUTCH SEAM CATCH
- OUTER GRADE NOTICEABLE
- FORMER LIFT SECTION ORDINARY TODAY

Then purchase:

OLD FORD STEP KEEPER
RIGHT HALF
PAIR INCOMPLETE
BF-W / 2
DOCUMENTED: PASSIVE EDGE COUNT / RETURN INDICATION
CURRENT RESPONSE UNKNOWN
6 C

Then:

DO NOT COMBINE WITH OVAL BECAUSE BOTH SOUND LIKE THRESHOLD THINGS.

Good.

Then:

RESERVE AFTER PURCHASE: 5 C

I stared at that longer.

Five.

Months ago five could feel like ceiling.

Now it was what remained after buying six-copper uncertainty because I decided I could absorb being wrong.

That was money changing without prices changing.

I liked that.

I hated that.

Mostly liked.

Passenger.

I had gone somewhere.

And brought something back.

# CHAPTER 377

## THE NEIGHBOR

The floor stopped being theoretical at breakfast.

A board squealed upstairs.

Not squeaked.

Squealed.

Long, offended sound like pig discovering taxes.

Everyone at table stopped.

Tavi looked ceiling.

"Ghost."

"Carpenter," Cala said.

"Carpenter ghost."

"Eat."

Upstairs, somebody hit something twice.

Then carpenter shouted, "Cala?"

Cala closed eyes.

Not fear.

The expression of woman whose morning had just developed invoice.

I stood.

"I'll go."

"Why?"

"Tall."

"That has never stopped you from making problem worse."

Fair.

We both went.

The upstairs room still looked like house interrupted mid-thought.

Wardrobe against wrong wall.

Temporary boards over repaired section.

Bed shifted.

Chest with artifacts locked and farther from work path than before.

Carpenter Brenna knelt beside exposed strip where one temporary board had come up.

Not Brenna from any grand plot.

Brenna the carpenter.

Carrow contained names without destiny.

She pointed into floor cavity.

"Found wet."

Cala crouched.

I stayed standing because crouching with one leg and crutches was possible but not contribution.

Dark stain ran along side of joist.

Not flood.

Damp.

Fresh enough wood darker.

"Roof?" Cala asked.

"No."

Brenna tapped pipe running through cavity.

Thin ceramic line, wrapped at joint with old gray compound.

"Wash line. Slow seep at collar. Floor damage wasn't only age."

There.

New information.

Not magical.

Still expensive-shaped.

"Active?" I asked.

"Small. See bead?"

Water gathered at underside joint.

One drop.

Wait.

Another.

"How long?"

"Can't know. Enough staining that not this morning."

Cala said nothing.

Brenna continued.

"Need line shut before I close floor. Plumber can reseal collar if ceramic isn't cracked. If cracked, section replacement."

"Today?"

"If you want floor closed today, yes."

Cala looked toward stairs.

Household arithmetic moved behind face.

I knew that face now.

Not my ledger.

Still my house.

Difference mattered.

"Who do you use?" Cala asked.

"I can send for Deren. He's two streets over on South Bell job. Might come midday."

"Price?"

"Not mine to quote."

Good.

Brenna had learned boundaries before meeting me.

"Can floor stay open?" I asked.

"Yes, if nobody walks this strip. Temporary cover can go back, but I won't finish over active seep."

"Damage worse if wait?"

"Slow seep. Could be same for days, could worsen. Shut wash line when not needed until looked at."

Cala nodded.

"Send for him."

Brenna sent apprentice.

Breakfast became late.

Lyssa arrived while I was carrying cups downstairs.

She had charcoal coat over arm, hair pinned badly, and expression of person who had been awake longer than day deserved.

"Why is carpenter apprentice running?"

"Plumbing."

"Of course."

She put coat down.

"My thing went through."

I stopped.

"Which thing?"

"All of it."

That narrowed nothing.

She sat.

Cala handed tea without asking.

Lyssa drank half before speaking.

Good sign she needed it.

"Second lot accepted. Twelve wraps, sixteen aprons, sixteen sleeve pairs. One apron tie had twist. I fixed it there. Everything else accepted. Nine copper."

There.

Nine.

Her money.

Not mine.

"And loops?"

"Accepted too. One copper rework paid separate."

Ten copper total today.

I did not say total.

I was learning.

"Good," I said.

She stared.

"That's it?"

"Do you want math?"

"No."

"Compliment?"

"Maybe."

"You're good at this."

She drank tea.

"Better."

Cala said, "Eat."

Lyssa took bread.

Tavi said, "Greg spent six copper on half bridge."

Traitor.

Lyssa pointed bread at me.

"I know."

"It doesn't work."

"We don't know that."

"Same thing."

"Not same thing."

"If I have half bread and don't know if bread works, it's bad bread."

"Bread has lower functional uncertainty."

Cala said, "Stop teaching him that sentence."

I had not.

Which was worse.

Lyssa laughed into tea.

Then upstairs board squealed again.

She looked ceiling.

"What's plumbing?"

We explained.

Her face shifted from amusement to practical concern.

"Can we use wash basin?"

"Cala is shutting line between uses until plumber comes."

"Bath?"

"Different line," Cala said.

"Good."

Priorities.

Lyssa finished bread, then pulled folded paper from coat pocket.

"Marra offered third lot."

I looked at paper, not reached.

"Want me involved?"

She hesitated.

"Later. Not now. I want sleep before deciding anything."

"Good."

"Don't say good like Hessa."

"I don't."

"You do sometimes."

I hated accumulation when used against me.

She went upstairs, saw open floor, came back down, and chose sofa.

Reasonable.

By midday plumber arrived.

Deren was narrow man with enormous mustache and habit of explaining every step to Cala even when she had not asked.

Not condescending exactly.

Compulsive narration.

"See, ceramic line itself is old kiln work, good kiln work actually, they don't fire these collars this thick anymore because everyone wants lighter runs and then everyone complains when lighter runs chatter, which is not same as leaking, mind you, chatter is support problem usually, unless pressure pulses, but this isn't pressure line, so here what we've got is collar bedding giving up. Maybe seal paste. Could be hair crack under wrap. Won't know until dry."

Cala listened.

I loved him immediately from safe distance.

Different speech register.

Man could drown city in explanation.

He shut line at lower valve.

Opened joint wrap carefully.

Gray compound flaked.

No magic flash.

No artifact.

Wet ceramic.

Deren dried it.

Waited.

Inspected with little brass mirror.

"No crack I can see. That's good. Could still have one where collar seats, but I don't think so. Bedding's gone brittle. Old seal paste gets like biscuit. Not good biscuit. Biscuit left in cupboard behind flour where nobody remembers it until winter."

Brenna said, "Price."

"Yes, yes. Reseat and seal, two copper including compound. If collar breaks while I remove it because hidden crack, replacement section adds three. I stop before replacement and ask."

Cala nodded.

"Two."

I stayed silent.

House money.

Not mine.

Deren looked at me.

"You live here?"

"Yes."

"Then you should know lower shutoff sticks quarter turn before close. Don't force past resistance because old stem. Back eighth, then forward."

Useful.

I wrote that down on household scrap, not artifact notebook.

Deren noticed crutches.

"You need clear floor path while this open."

"Yes."

"Brenna, your cover has lip."

Brenna looked.

"Temporary."

"Temporary still catches."

She looked at my crutch tips.

Then at cover.

"Fair."

She beveled edge with plane.

Thirty seconds.

No committee.

No special speech.

Just neighbor-scale competence.

I liked Carrow.

Annoying realization.

Deren resealed collar.

Two copper from Cala.

No hidden crack.

He left line dry for one bell, then reopened slowly.

No bead.

Wait.

Still no bead.

Brenna refused to close floor immediately anyway.

"I want it under pressure until late afternoon."

Deren approved.

"If dry then, floor closes. If damp, send boy."

Cala asked whether two-copper repair had any guarantee.

Deren launched explanation involving workmanship, old ceramic, new seal compound, freeze risk that Carrow apparently did not have often enough to matter, and one customer who used pipe as shelf support.

Eventually answer: if his new seal fails within thirty days under ordinary household use, labor redo no charge; material fault outside joint separate.

Cala made him write it.

I definitely liked her.

Afternoon had no assignment.

No work.

No market.

No training.

Five copper remained five.

Step Keeper stayed locked.

This was apparently possible.

I sat outside because upstairs smelled of wet wood and seal compound.

Jessa passed with oil ledger under arm.

"Bell Ford."

I looked at her.

"What?"

"Sella said you went."

"News travels."

"You bought bridge junk."

"News travels too far."

She sat on low wall without invitation.

"What was Ford like?"

Question surprised me.

Not what bought.

Not road.

Place.

"Smaller. More trees. Blue tile on public roofs. Bridge has bells."

"Bells?"

"Lots. Wind and water."

"Annoying?"

"Actually no."

"Huh."

She looked down street.

"Never been."

"Really?"

"Why would I?"

"Because road continues."

"That's a terrible reason."

"Worked for me."

"You had free ride."

"Also true."

Jessa picked lint from sleeve.

"Olin went once. Hated smoked river eel. Talked about it for six years like town personally cooked wrong."

"Did he try other food?"

"No."

"Then his sample is weak."

"Tell him."

"Absolutely not."

She smiled.

Then said, "You going again?"

I had not thought beyond object.

"Maybe."

Maybe again.

Expensive word.

But travel version.

"Why?"

"I don't know."

Jessa nodded like that was better answer than expected.

"If you do, bring eel so I can tell Olin he's wrong."

"You can go."

"I work."

"So do I. Sometimes."

"Your employment model is unsettling."

Fair.

She left.

Naro arrived ten minutes later.

Of course.

No jar.

Improvement.

Mevi with him.

Naro held stick.

Ordinary stick.

I checked anyway.

"We came to see Bell Ford rock."

Tavi had advertised.

"It's Tavi's."

"He said it has stripe."

"It does."

"Why?"

"Rock."

"That's not why."

"Minerals. Probably."

"Which?"

"Don't know."

Naro sighed.

My reputation continued correcting downward.

Healthy.

Tavi came out with rock.

Children examined it like state treasure.

Mevi sat on step.

"Mom says thank you again for drain thing."

"Sella did actual work."

"Mom thanked them too."

Good.

"Any more cases?"

"Don't know. Crew put screen. We weren't supposed go look."

"Did you?"

Naro said, "Near."

Mevi said, "He went near."

"Not to mouth."

"You went to red wall."

"Wall is not mouth."

I held up hand.

"I don't need exact legal defense."

Naro looked disappointed.

"Did you touch anything?"

"No."

"Good."

"I learned."

"Better."

He considered whether compliment acceptable.

Then Tavi announced Bell Ford had sun gnats that ate flower shine.

I had told him once.

Now ecology propagated.

Naro immediately asked whether flower becomes invisible.

"No," I said.

"Then what's shine?"

"Ask Rell."

"Who's Rell?"

"Woman in Bell Ford?"

"She lives there?"

I paused.

I did not know.

"Woman I met on trip."

"Then how ask?"

"You don't."

"Bad system."

Mevi laughed.

The four kids went inside after Cala approved.

House acquired noise.

I stayed step.

Neighbor.

That was what day felt like.

Not owner.

Not appraiser.

Not worker.

Not teacher.

Person who knew shutoff now.

Person whose crutch path got beveled because plumber noticed.

Person people asked what Bell Ford looked like.

Person children visited because another child had rock.

First life had neighborhoods too.

I had mostly passed through them.

Even when lived somewhere, rank and work and travel made place temporary in my head.

There were exceptions.

People.

Always people.

I remembered one old inn where owner kept my preferred cup for three years even though I appeared maybe twice each winter.

Blue glaze.

Cracked handle.

Terrible cup.

Mine because she said so.

Then inn burned during border raid.

Owner survived.

Cup did not.

I had been more upset about cup than reasonable.

Objects become evidence that place expected you back.

Dangerous thought around current chest.

I did not write it.

Late afternoon Brenna checked pipe.

Dry.

She pressed cloth to joint.

Dry.

Floor closed.

Actual repaired boards went down.

Not all. One section needed finish tomorrow because replacement plank had to settle after cut.

But path restored.

Wardrobe still wrong wall until tomorrow.

Cala asked total carpenter cost.

Brenna reminded her original quote plus extra plank only, plumbing separate. No surprise labor for waiting because she had other work downstairs trim while plumber worked.

I did not need numbers.

Not my ledger.

Again.

At dinner Lyssa woke from sofa looking less dead.

Marra's third-lot paper remained folded.

She did not open it.

Good.

Cala made soup.

Naro and Mevi had gone home.

Tavi put Bell Ford rock beside bowl.

"No," Cala said.

He moved it to windowsill.

Lyssa looked at me.

"Did you test bridge half?"

"No."

"Are you sick?"

"Resting it."

"It needs rest?"

"I need rest from myself."

She nodded.

"That I believe."

After dinner she finally unfolded Marra paper.

I did not lean.

Growth.

She read silently.

Longer than expected.

Then handed it to me.

Permission.

Third lot was not simply bigger.

Different.

South Hall wanted repeat service goods plus first **heat-cloth** trial.

Eight aprons.

Eight sleeve pairs.

Six wraps.

Four heat-cloths using supplied treated weave.

Twelve copper accepted return.

Six days.

Heat-cloth seams had separate stitch spacing and edge-fold requirements.

One sample piece supplied.

If heat-cloth sample failed, service-goods portion could still be accepted separately at listed subrate.

Marra had finally separated failure categories.

Good contract evolution.

Lyssa said, "I don't know treated weave."

"Then question is whether you want learn it for this price and structure."

"I know."

I shut up.

She tapped paper.

"I think I do."

"Okay."

"But I want one day before I say yes."

"Okay."

"Stop being agreeable."

"No."

Better.

She smiled.

"There."

Cala said from stove, "Tomorrow is still a day."

Lyssa folded paper.

"Tomorrow."

No acceptance yet.

No new income.

No rush.

House had dry pipe.

Floor mostly closed.

Lyssa had ten copper earned from completed second lot plus rework, though exact private total remained hers.

I had five.

Chest had one more questionable object.

Tavi had free rock.

Maybe he won economy.

Before bed I checked lower shutoff.

Not to operate.

To know location.

Quarter-turn resistance point visible by old scratch.

Back eighth, then forward.

Deren's words.

Useful knowledge that did not belong in artifact book.

I wrote on small card and tucked beside household maintenance papers:

UPSTAIRS WASH LINE
LOWER SHUTOFF STICKS BEFORE CLOSED
DO NOT FORCE
BACK 1/8 THEN FORWARD
DEREN RESEALED COLLAR
30-DAY LABOR REDO IF NEW SEAL FAILS UNDER ORDINARY USE

Cala read it.

"Good."

Not Hessa good.

House good.

Different.

I went upstairs.

Repaired path held.

No squeal.

Wardrobe remained wrong.

Chest locked.

I did not open it.

Outside, somebody argued about onions.

Cart rolled over uneven stone.

A child called another child liar with tremendous conviction.

Carrow.

Home, apparently.

Neighbor.

That one had happened slowly.

# CHAPTER 378

## THE USHER

The theatre sent for me because somebody else had fallen down.

Not badly.

This mattered.

Nessa's runner found me outside the house while Brenna was putting final finish board upstairs.

"West House," runner said. "Tonight."

"What happened?"

"Maro turned ankle on gallery stair."

"Bad?"

"Swollen. Walking with help. Nessa says not broken unless Maro is lying."

"Maro qualified?"

"To lie? Very."

Fair.

Runner handed note.

GREG

NEED HOUSE ACCESS COVER TONIGHT.
NOT ROUTE TEACHING.
NOT LAMP WORK.
DOORS / WEST GALLERY / LATE EXIT.
2 C.
ARRIVE BEFORE FIRST BELL IF AVAILABLE.

NESSA

Specificity had become threatening.

They knew enough to say what I was not doing.

I liked that.

Two copper.

Five to seven.

If I accepted.

I looked upstairs.

Brenna had floor.

Cala had house.

Lyssa was out giving Marra answer on third lot.

Tavi had been told not to stand on finish board approximately nine times and had so far obeyed eight.

"Yes," I said.

Runner nodded and left.

No negotiation.

Two copper was fair for evening house access because theatre had paid that before.

Not every known rate required performance.

I went inside.

Cala looked at note.

"Go."

"Brenna finishing."

"I live here."

Right.

"Tavi."

"Also lives here."

"That's my concern."

Tavi shouted from stairs, "I didn't touch it."

Nobody had accused him.

Concerning.

Cala pointed door.

"Work."

I went.

West House had crowd before first bell.

Not main-stage crowd.

Smaller performance, apparently, but West House entry had become awkward because usual west-gallery usher Maro sat on stool with ankle wrapped and face furious.

"You fell?" I asked.

"Stair attacked."

"Witnesses?"

"Traitors."

Nessa appeared.

"He missed step carrying programs."

"Programs shifted."

"Gravity remains under investigation."

Maro glared.

Nessa handed me house slate.

"Your job."

Three lines:

WEST GALLERY ENTRY
AISLE SEATING / CANE-CRUTCH CLEARANCE
LATE EXIT AFTER MASK SCENE

I looked at second.

"Because me?"

"Because you notice it and Maro normally does. Also Rell complained about our aisle last month."

Rell?

"Bell Ford Rell?"

"What? No. Rell Vanno. Different Rell."

Good.

Names remained non-destiny.

"What exactly is cane-crutch clearance?"

"Don't let people stack bags or stools in aisle. Seat mobility aids without creating trip hazard. If someone needs slower stair, hold flow. If someone cannot use gallery stair safely, offer floor section if available. Don't decide for them. Ask."

That last part landed.

"Late exit?"

"Mask scene uses dark transition. We don't release west gallery until aisle lamps return. People always think they can see enough. They cannot."

"And doors?"

"Open at first bell. Count only capacity marks, not people identity. Red mark full. No standing behind back rail."

House work.

Simple.

Until people arrived.

People make every simple system creative.

First was family of five who wanted four seats because youngest "doesn't really sit."

"Then youngest needs space somewhere," I said.

Father said, "He'll be on lap."

Child was maybe eight.

Large eight.

Mother looked tired enough to leave family at door.

"Five bodies need five capacity marks," I said.

"He's not using seat."

"Capacity isn't seat count."

Father started argument.

Mother said, "Buy five."

Father bought five.

Marriage survived.

Second problem: woman with cane wanted west gallery because sister already there.

Stair narrow.

I asked, "Want arm support, slower clear stair, or floor alternative?"

She looked at me.

Then crutches.

"Slower stair. Don't touch me unless I ask."

"Good."

I held incoming flow at bottom.

She climbed.

Slow.

Fine.

People behind waited thirty seconds and somehow survived.

Third problem: boy put folded stool in aisle.

"Move it."

"My grandmother needs it."

Grandmother said, "I need it under me, idiot."

Boy moved stool.

Different family survived.

By first bell west gallery almost full.

I liked ushering less than route teaching.

No.

Different.

Route teaching meant telling workers what pattern already known.

Ushering meant watching strangers invent exceptions.

Nessa passed once.

"Status?"

"Three marks open. Aisle clear. One cane user upstairs by choice. Maro still victim of gravity."

"Good."

Maro from stool said, "Hostile workplace."

Nessa kept walking.

Performance began.

Mirror-mask rehearsal from Three Reeds day had become actual scene.

I noticed because glare did not hit west gallery.

Theatre's replacement shade apparently arrived.

Good.

My artifact not needed.

Also good.

Three Reeds had solved temporary need, not captured institution.

I stood by rear entry.

Actors argued inheritance under masks.

One still breathed before lies.

Nessa had kept it.

Audience laughed first time.

Oh.

Maybe actor was right.

Art remained suspicious.

Halfway through first scene, woman in second row started coughing.

Not dramatic.

Then more.

She stood.

I opened rear door quietly.

Her companion followed.

Outside landing she leaned rail.

"Air," she said.

"Want water?"

"No. Just air."

I did not diagnose.

Companion rubbed back.

After minute woman said, "Smoke smell."

I smelled.

Nothing.

Then faint.

Not theatre smoke.

Hot dust?

Oil?

I looked inside.

No visible haze.

Could be costume lamp.

Could be street.

Could be imagination.

"Stay here," I said.

To companion: "I'm getting house lead."

Nessa was across room.

I did not cross audience.

I sent runner.

Thirty seconds later Jori arrived instead.

"Report."

"Patron coughing. Says smoke smell. I now smell faint hot dust or oil at west rear landing. No visible haze."

Jori sniffed.

Face changed slightly.

"Yes."

He looked up.

West rear aisle lamp nearest landing flickered once.

Not out.

Flicker.

Jori said, "Hold door. Don't announce."

He moved.

I held.

Patron said, "Is something burning?"

"Jori is checking."

"Should we leave?"

Question.

Authority.

"You can leave now if you want. Stair is lit. If house needs broader exit, we'll direct it."

She nodded.

They left down stair.

No panic.

Jori returned with Nessa and small red cloth marker.

"Rear lamp feed collar heating," Jori said. "Not flame. We are isolating west rear lamp. Front aisle lamps stay."

Nessa asked, "Can gallery remain?"

Jori: "Yes if rear fixture dark and smell clears. If not, clear gallery."

Nessa looked at me.

"Late exit rule changes. Rear lamp unavailable. At mask dark transition, west gallery holds until front aisle lamps full and runner confirms stair landing lit from lower fixture."

"Understood."

There.

Actual consequence.

No artifact.

No heroics.

System degraded one piece and adapted.

Jori isolated lamp.

Rear corner darker.

Hot smell faded.

Audience mostly never noticed.

Performance continued.

Maro whispered, "You get all interesting shifts."

"You fell carrying paper."

"Exactly."

At interval, west gallery emptied partially.

Cane user came down slowly.

Same choice.

I held flow.

A man behind her sighed loudly.

She stopped halfway and turned.

"You want go first?"

Man said, "No, no."

"Then breathe quieter."

I liked her.

Not because dry comeback.

Because she had earned it.

At bottom she told me, "Stair rail is too thick near turn. Can't close hand."

I looked.

Rail widened around decorative carved joint.

For my crutch use, irrelevant.

For cane plus rail grip, maybe not.

"Want me to pass that on?"

"Yes."

I wrote exact:

WEST GALLERY STAIR RAIL
TURN JOINT TOO THICK FOR FULL HAND CLOSURE
PATRON REPORT

Not Greg discovers accessibility.

Patron tells theatre.

Important.

At interval Nessa read note.

"Maro?"

Maro said, "Heard before."

Nessa turned.

"Where recorded?"

Maro looked guilty.

"I thought it was complaint."

"It is complaint. That's why record."

"I know now."

Nessa's face did not change.

"Tomorrow you find prior complaints."

Maro sighed.

"Ankle hurts."

"Tomorrow your ankle can supervise paper."

Longer answer would have been kinder.

Nessa was not kinder.

Different voice.

Second half started.

West rear lamp remained dark.

Front aisle lamps enough during normal scenes.

Mask scene approached.

I knew because actors changed costume and runner whispered, "Dark transition next."

I checked stair landing from top.

Lower fixture visible.

But not enough to trust after full dark transition until eyes adjusted.

Rule: hold.

Scene ended.

Stage darkened.

Audience moved instinctively.

People always do.

I put arm across doorway, not touching anyone.

"West gallery holds. Aisle lamps first."

Someone said, "I can see."

Of course.

"Hold."

Front aisle lamps came up.

Rear remained dark.

Runner at stair bottom raised white card.

Landing lit.

"West gallery clear. Slow at turn."

People moved.

No fall.

No rush.

No problem.

Which is what good house work looked like.

Late arrivals came during next scene.

Two women, one man, and child asleep on shoulder.

Only three capacity marks open.

Four bodies.

"Floor section may have room," I whispered.

Man said, "Child asleep. Doesn't need seat."

Again.

Capacity wasn't furniture.

I explained.

One woman looked annoyed.

Other said, "We'll split."

Two gallery, two floor.

"You don't have to split," I said. "Runner can check four together floor."

They chose that.

Good.

My job was not enforce misery when alternative existed.

Near final scene, Maro asked, "You want this regularly?"

I looked at him.

"Your job?"

"Not my whole job. West gallery part."

"Are you retiring from stairs after one ankle?"

"No. Nessa needs more cover people."

That was different.

"Why ask me?"

"Because you don't get weird when people need time."

I almost laughed.

"I get weird about many things."

"Not that."

He nodded toward cane woman now seated again.

"Half house staff sees delay and thinks problem. You see delay and think delay."

That sat oddly.

Not compliment I expected.

First life had not made me patient.

Second body maybe did.

No.

Second life did.

Body helped.

People helped more.

"Maybe," I said.

"That's not yes."

"Correct."

"Nessa hates maybe."

"Nessa survives."

"Usually."

Performance ended.

Applause.

House lights.

West gallery exit ordinary now except rear lamp dark.

I kept people from stacking at stair turn.

Cane woman descended last by choice.

At bottom she said, "Thank you for not grabbing me."

"You told me not to."

She gave look.

"You'd be surprised."

I probably would not.

But I didn't say that.

After house clear, Jori brought rear lamp collar to table.

Not for me to inspect.

I happened to be there while he logged.

Heat discoloration around feed collar.

No flame damage.

He tagged fixture out of service.

"Same feed branch as Ch351 outage?" I almost asked.

No.

I did not know.

And chapters did not exist in world.

More importantly, similar infrastructure did not mean same cause.

I asked instead, "Known issue?"

Jori said, "Not this collar."

Good.

"Replacement?"

"Tomorrow."

"Cause?"

"Tomorrow."

Also good.

Nessa paid me two copper.

Five to seven.

Actual.

"Maro says you may need more cover people."

She looked at Maro.

Maro looked betrayed.

"I said maybe."

"He said maybe," I confirmed.

Nessa considered.

"We do."

"Regular?"

"Not standing schedule yet. We need three more people who can cover doors, gallery, late exit, route reset. Training paid one copper first half, two once cleared for independent house shift."

There.

Opportunity.

Not mine automatically.

"You're offering?"

"I'm asking whether you want clearance. You already know pieces. Formalize remaining."

"Would that replace route work?"

"No. Different call list."

"How often?"

"Unknown. More during festival months. Less after."

"Can decline calls?"

"Yes. Too many declines, we stop calling."

Reasonable.

"Any exclusivity?"

Nessa stared.

"For usher cover? No."

"Then yes."

"Training tomorrow?"

Too fast.

I had no guaranteed work.

Seven copper now.

Could train.

But Lyssa maybe third lot, floor finishing, life.

"What time?"

"Third bell afternoon. One half."

"Yes."

Nessa wrote my name.

Not contract.

Call list.

Small thing.

Access.

Reputation becoming opportunity without becoming destiny.

I liked that too.

Maro said, "Congratulations. Now people yell at you about seats."

"Already happened."

"Then you're trained."

Nessa said, "No."

Maro sighed.

"See?"

I walked home after dark.

Seven copper in purse.

No artifact purchase.

I passed Tool Lane and did not turn.

Heroic.

At house, upstairs floor finished.

Wardrobe back original wall.

Path restored.

Tavi was lying on floor exactly where temporary boards had been.

"Why?"

"Testing."

"Get up."

"It's good."

"Excellent report. Get up."

He got up.

Cala said, "Dry. Brenna done."

Good.

House problem closed enough.

Lyssa sat table with treated-weave sample.

Not sewing.

Looking.

"You accepted?"

"Yes."

Brief.

Off-axis.

Good.

"Third lot?"

"Yes. I told Marra I want heat-cloth sample acceptance before I cut remaining treated weave. She agreed."

Smart.

"So you make one first?"

"One sample cloth. If accepted, other three. Service goods separate."

"Good."

She narrowed eyes.

"Hessa."

"Damn it."

She smiled.

"How was theatre?"

"Maro lost war with stair. Rear aisle lamp overheated. Nobody died. I got added to house-cover training."

"You have a job?"

"Absolutely not."

"You have several jobs that refuse to admit they're jobs."

"That's different."

"How?"

I thought.

"Less commitment."

"More confusion."

"Also true."

I put two copper on table briefly.

"Seven."

She nodded.

Not celebration.

Just known.

Then pointed at treated weave.

"Don't touch."

"Wasn't going to."

"You were looking."

"Looking remains legal."

"Not with your face."

Apparently my face continued infrastructure problem.

I left her material alone.

Notebook upstairs.

CASH: 7 C

VALE: 23 C

WEST HOUSE COVER: 2 C

Then:

WEST GALLERY
- CAPACITY = BODIES, NOT SEATS
- ASK MOBILITY SUPPORT; DO NOT ASSUME
- HOLD FLOW FOR SLOW STAIR
- AISLE CLEAR
- DARK TRANSITION: RELEASE ONLY AFTER LIGHT CONFIRMATION

Then patron report:

STAIR RAIL TURN JOINT TOO THICK FOR FULL HAND CLOSURE
PASSED TO NESSA

Then:

REAR AISLE LAMP FEED COLLAR HEATED
ISOLATED BY JORI
CAUSE UNKNOWN
DO NOT CONNECT TO OTHER FEED PROBLEMS WITHOUT EVIDENCE

Then opportunity:

HOUSE COVER TRAINING TOMORROW THIRD BELL
1 C TRAINING HALF
2 C INDEPENDENT SHIFT AFTER CLEARANCE
CALL LIST / CAN DECLINE / NO EXCLUSIVITY

Seven copper.

Five after Step Keeper had already become seven again.

Not because purchase punished me into scramble.

Because work existed.

Small work.

People knew me.

I knew routes.

I knew house.

I knew when not to grab somebody on stairs.

That last one should have been obvious.

Apparently obvious skills still had market value if enough people failed them.

I closed notebook.

Usher.

Not glamorous.

Useful.

# CHAPTER 379

## THE BROKER

Vale had a chair for me.

That was how I knew something was wrong.

Not wrong dangerous.

Wrong expensive.

His office usually had chairs because offices did, but the one across from desk had been moved half a pace left, leaving clean space for crutches against wall.

Prepared.

I distrusted preparation when directed at me.

Vale looked up.

"Sit."

"How much does chair cost?"

"Nothing yet."

Worse.

I sat.

Vale pushed folded paper across desk.

Did not say debt.

Also worse.

"I have a client with a problem," he said.

"Congratulations."

"You know someone."

"I know several people now. It's getting out of hand."

He ignored.

Paper stayed folded.

"Client bought a retired mill component from North Secondary disposal. Not magical component, before you become interested."

I became less interested.

"Counterframe guide assembly. Iron, bronze shoes, two stone seats. Heavy. Awkward. Purchased for rebuild project."

"And?"

"One stone seat has old yard mark K-fourteen family."

Interest returned.

Not K-14-6 necessarily.

Family.

My thumb-sized counterload rest stone sat boxed at home.

"What exact mark?"

"K-fourteen-nine."

There.

Different item.

Same family maybe.

Could mean nothing beyond yard series.

Vale watched face.

Annoying man.

"Client wants what?"

"Information. Specifically someone who can tell him whether retaining the stone seat creates disposal, registration, or resale complication before he cuts assembly apart."

"I cannot."

"I know."

Good.

"Then why chair?"

"Because you know Mara."

I leaned back.

Ah.

Not appraiser.

Not expert.

Connection.

"You know Mara too."

"Professionally. She does not owe me casual interruption."

"Neither does she owe me."

"Correct."

Vale folded hands.

"But she has answered bounded questions for you before. Client will pay for a proper inspection if she accepts. I want introduction, not free opinion."

That was cleaner.

"Why not send client to her?"

"I did."

"And?"

"Her board is full through next six days."

"Then wait six days."

"Client intends cutting frame tomorrow because shop space costs money."

There.

Time pressure.

Not emergency.

Money pressure.

"Could store assembly?"

"At cost."

"How much?"

"Client's problem."

Right.

I looked folded paper.

"What are you offering me?"

"One copper for introduction if Mara agrees to hear request. Two additional copper if she accepts a paid inspection within client's two-day window."

Three potential.

Not for expertise.

For access.

Brokerage.

I disliked word before thinking it.

"No payment tied to her answer?"

"No."

"No payment tied to client buying, keeping, cutting, selling?"

"No."

"No percentage?"

"No."

"Good."

Vale almost smiled.

"You have learned something."

"Mostly suspicion."

"Useful curriculum."

I unfolded paper.

Client: Orsen Pell.

Workshop: East Mill Court.

Assembly: retired counterframe guide, disposal lot number, purchase date, declared mundane frame hardware with two stone seats retained in assembly.

One seat mark transcribed `K-14-9`.

Question requested:

DOES MARKED STONE SEAT REQUIRE SPECIAL HANDLING / REGISTRATION / INTACT RETENTION BEFORE DISASSEMBLY?

No claim it worked.

No claim magical.

No request valuation.

Good.

"Why me instead of paying Mara rush rate?"

"Because she has not offered rush rate."

"Good answer."

"I occasionally have them."

"Do I owe debt service if this works?"

"No. Cash task. Separate."

Important.

"Why?"

Vale looked irritated.

"Because not every useful thing you do for me must become debt accounting."

I stared.

"Who are you and what happened to Vale?"

"Take task or leave."

There he was.

"I'll ask Mara whether she wants request. I will not tell her client needs answer, only that client has time window."

"Fine."

"I will not pressure."

"Fine."

"If she says no, one copper still if she heard request?"

"If you actually deliver request, yes."

"If she says come back six days?"

"One copper."

"If she refers someone else?"

Vale paused.

"One copper for introduction to Mara. If referral leads to accepted inspection within two days, second two copper applies."

"Write that."

He did.

I liked Cala's influence on me.

Or hated.

Same result.

I left with paper.

Seven copper remained seven.

Potential ten.

More interesting: K-14-9 existed.

I did not go home for K-14-6.

Did not need it.

Also carrying own related object into someone else's inspection request would muddy everything.

I wrote mentally:

DO NOT MAKE YOUR COLLECTION THE CENTER OF OTHER PEOPLE'S PROBLEMS.

Good rule.

Possibly insulting rule.

Mara was at rear bench when I arrived.

Not closed-housing bench.

Different room.

Open windows, two apprentices sorting tags.

She saw me.

"If this is yours, no."

"Paid request. Not mine."

That changed her attention.

"Who?"

"Orsen Pell, East Mill Court. Retired counterframe guide assembly. One stone seat marked K-14-9. He wants to know whether special handling, registration, or intact retention matters before disassembly. Two-day window because shop space. Vale is intermediary."

Mara held hand.

Paper.

I gave it.

She read.

"Did you see assembly?"

"No."

"Good."

Interesting standard.

"Did Vale?"

"Paper doesn't say."

"Did client remove stone?"

"Paper says retained in assembly."

She read again.

"K-fourteen is yard family, not artifact class."

There.

Bounded information.

My K-14-6 note already treated family broadly, but useful reinforcement.

"Nine could be ordinary seat. Six could be ordinary seat. Mark alone doesn't establish active behavior."

"Understood."

"You understand when supervised."

Hessa had competition.

Mara looked toward apprentice.

"Siv, tomorrow second bell?"

Apprentice Siv checked slate.

"You have South Lock fittings."

"Third bell?"

"Merren inspection at third."

"Fourth?"

"Lunch."

Mara stared.

Siv did not move.

"You told me to write lunch."

"I did."

"So lunch."

Mara sighed.

Different voice.

Apprentice protected specialist from herself.

Useful.

"Fifth bell," Mara said.

Siv checked.

"Open half bell before East Gate housing."

Mara looked at request.

"Half bell enough for handling classification if assembly accessible and papers present. Not valuation. Not function certification. Two copper inspection fee plus travel one copper. Three total. Client accepts before I go."

I nodded.

"Want me carry answer?"

"Yes. Exact."

She wrote:

TOMORROW FIFTH BELL
HALF-BELL HANDLING / PAPER INSPECTION ONLY
3 C TOTAL INCLUDING TRAVEL
ASSEMBLY ACCESSIBLE, STONE NOT REMOVED
PURCHASE / DISPOSAL PAPERS PRESENT
NO VALUATION
NO FUNCTION CERTIFICATION

Then below:

IF ACCEPTED, SEND CONFIRMATION BY END OF TODAY.

"Anything else?"

"Don't tell him K-fourteen means anything useful."

"You just told me it means yard family."

"That's all."

"I won't."

She handed paper.

"And Greg."

"Yes?"

"Do not bring yours."

I stared.

"I wasn't going to."

"Good."

Damn it.

I left.

Vale's office would have been easy.

But task was introduction and accepted inspection within two-day window.

Client needed accept.

I could route through Vale.

Or go East Mill Court.

Paper had address.

Which was intended?

I returned Vale first.

He was not surprised.

"Three copper inspection," I said. "Tomorrow fifth bell, half bell, handling and papers only. Assembly accessible, stone stays in, purchase and disposal papers present. No valuation, no function certification. Confirmation by end today."

Vale nodded.

"Good."

"Client needs receive."

"I'll send runner."

"Then my task waits."

"Yes."

"One copper earned?"

"Yes."

He paid one.

Eight.

Not ten yet.

Correct.

"You can leave," Vale said.

"I want to know if accepts."

"Curiosity or money?"

"Both."

"At least honest."

I sat in prepared chair.

Vale worked.

I discovered watching someone do debt ledgers was worse than owing debt.

Maybe.

No.

Owing still worse.

Ten minutes.

Twenty.

Runner returned breathing hard.

"Accepted."

Vale looked up.

"Terms?"

Runner handed note.

ORSEN PELL ACCEPTS 3 C
ASSEMBLY REMAINS INTACT / ACCESSIBLE
PAPERS READY
TOMORROW FIFTH BELL

Vale handed me two copper.

Ten.

Simple.

Three copper earned because I knew who to ask and did not distort request.

That was unsettling.

"Do I attend inspection?" I asked.

"No."

"Good."

Vale looked at me.

"Unless Mara asks."

"She won't."

"Probably not."

I folded my copy of terms.

"Does client know I got paid?"

"My fee arrangement with you is my expense. His three copper goes Mara directly."

"Does he pay you?"

"Yes."

"How much?"

"Not your problem."

Fair.

I stood.

Vale said, "There may be more of this."

I stopped.

"What is this?"

"Knowing who actually knows things."

Dangerous compliment.

"I know very few people."

"More than before. More importantly, you increasingly know limits of what they know."

That landed harder.

"Don't turn me into referral desk."

"I don't intend to. Referral desks are cheaper."

"Comforting."

"I intend to call when mismatch costs more than your fee."

There.

Vale.

Not kindness.

Economics.

Better.

"Case by case," I said.

"Obviously."

I left with ten copper.

Not rich.

But back to pre-Step-Keeper cash after two days.

Purchase had not vanished.

I still owned object.

Money recovered through network.

That felt different from earning by lifting, tallying, teaching, watching lamp, ushering.

Information routing.

First life had plenty.

At higher rank, people constantly asked who could solve what.

I had treated that as command structure.

Sometimes it was.

Sometimes I had been doing this and not noticed because nobody paid three copper equivalent for introductions when I was S-class.

They paid in favors.

Worse currency.

Harder ledger.

I stopped at bread stall.

Did not buy because bit continuity remained metaphysical curse.

No.

Because I wasn't hungry.

Important distinction.

At home, Lyssa had sample heat-cloth half assembled.

Treated weave looked ordinary until light hit angle, then fibers had faint bronze dullness.

Not glow.

Not evidence magic.

Just material.

I stayed two steps away.

She noticed.

"Why are you hovering?"

"I'm not touching."

"You can sit."

"Closer?"

"Yes."

Progress.

I sat opposite.

"How does it handle?"

"Stiffer across fold. Worse on needle turn. Edge wants crawl if I pull same as normal weave."

Work knowledge.

"So different tension?"

"Maybe. Don't help."

"Was asking."

"Your asking has elbows."

I did not know what that meant.

Probably accurate.

She sewed three stitches.

Stopped.

Pulled back one.

"Marra said treated side must face marked side of fold."

"Which is treated side?"

She pointed tiny black thread woven into selvage.

"This edge mark tells orientation."

"Does she know why?"

"She said South Hall kitchen knows. I need make it correctly, not become kitchen."

Excellent.

I said nothing.

Lyssa looked suspicious.

"You agree too hard."

"I'm learning restraint."

"No you're not."

Fair.

Cala came in with market basket.

"You got paid?"

News again.

"How?"

"Vale's runner passed me. Said you were sitting in office waiting for money."

Carrow surveillance network was mostly people walking streets.

"Three copper."

"For?"

"Introducing Vale's client request to Mara. She accepted paid inspection."

Cala set basket down.

"You charged friend for knowing friend?"

There.

Social discomfort.

"Vale paid me. Mara gets her own three copper from client. I did not take from Mara."

"Did Mara know you were paid?"

I paused.

No.

Not stated.

Cala saw pause.

"Hm."

That hm cost more than copper.

"Should she?"

"I don't know."

Annoying honest answer.

"You think yes."

"I think if somebody brought me work because another person paid them, I'd want know if it changed what they said."

"It didn't."

"Then telling is easy."

Damn.

Lyssa did not look up from sewing.

"She's right."

"You weren't invited."

"I'm at table."

"Hostile table."

She smiled.

I considered.

Vale paid one for delivering request, two if inspection accepted.

That created incentive to make Mara accept.

I had deliberately not pressured.

But Mara did not know incentive existed.

That mattered.

Brokerage without disclosure could become manipulation even if this time clean.

First life favors again.

Hidden incentives everywhere.

I hated discovering ethics after getting paid.

"I'll tell her," I said.

Cala unpacked onions.

"Good."

Not Hessa.

Not house good either.

Maybe Cala good.

Too many goods.

I went back.

Mara saw me and looked immediately annoyed.

"What now?"

"Disclosure."

That got eyebrow.

"Vale paid me one copper to deliver request, plus two if you accepted inspection within two days. I did not tell you before asking. I should have."

Mara stared.

Long enough.

"Did Vale condition payment on my conclusion?"

"No."

"On fee?"

"No."

"On accepting within two days."

"Yes."

"Did you pressure my schedule?"

"I gave window and said client had shop-space reason. You chose fifth bell."

"Did you tell Vale anything beyond my written terms?"

"No."

She considered.

"Then inspection stands."

Relief.

"Future?"

"Tell me before I answer if your pay depends on yes."

"Yes."

"If you're paid flat to carry request, I don't care. If bonus depends on my acceptance, I care."

Specific.

Good.

"Understood."

"And don't become broker who thinks every friendship is inventory."

There.

"I was worried about that."

"Then keep worrying enough."

Mara returned work.

I did not apologize six times.

Growth.

Outside, I updated note:

REFERRAL RULE
DISCLOSE IF MY PAY CHANGES BASED ON SPECIALIST SAYING YES
FLAT DELIVERY FEE LESS IMPORTANT, BUT DO NOT HIDE IF RELEVANT
NO PAY TIED TO RESULT / VALUATION / PURCHASE
FRIENDSHIP IS NOT INVENTORY

Last line Mara's.

I hated it because good.

At third bell I had theatre training.

Not chapter center.

Still happened.

Nessa spent half bell on capacity slate, emergency exits, patron disputes, cash handoff, lost-property procedure, and exact difference between house lead and door cover authority.

No incident.

No drama.

I passed enough to be cleared for ordinary doors/gallery cover, but not cash box close and not emergency house lead.

Paid one copper.

Eleven.

Nessa added my name to call list.

There.

Durable.

"Don't make me regret it," she said.

"Specific failure category?"

"All."

Maro, ankle still wrapped, said, "She's supportive."

Nessa ignored.

I left with eleven copper.

One more than before Step Keeper.

Interesting.

Not because artifact produced value.

Because city did.

Relationships did.

Skills did.

Access did.

That distinction mattered if I wanted collection without becoming idiot who expected every object to pay for itself immediately.

At home, Step Keeper remained locked.

I opened chest only to put papers in proper folder.

Did not touch housing.

There it was.

Six-copper question.

Beside other questions.

Chest getting crowded.

Soon storage needed answer.

Not tonight.

I counted copper once.

Eleven.

Vale twenty-three.

Then wrote one final line:

ACCESS HAS VALUE.

Crossed it out.

Too broad.

Wrote:

KNOWING WHO TO ASK CAN BE WORK.

Better.

Then below:

DON'T SELL THE PERSON.
SELL THE ROUTING.

I stared.

Still sounded like broker.

Because apparently I had been one.

Broker.

Case by case.

# CHAPTER 380

## THE DELEGATE

The trip was supposed to be two nights.

That was the first lie.

Nobody meant it as lie.

Those were usually better.

Sella found me at breakfast with road paper folded into quarters and mud already drying on boots.

"You free three days?"

I looked at Cala.

Cala looked at me.

Tavi looked between us like auction had started.

"Define free," I said.

"Not dead. Not contracted. Can leave tomorrow morning."

"That's aggressive definition."

Sella put paper on table.

Blue-hook drain crew mark at top.

Under it, Hanging Road maintenance seal.

Under that, Bell Ford transit office.

Three systems on one page.

Interesting.

"What happened?"

"Nothing yet."

Worse.

Sella tapped first line.

"Road reopening trial goes farther than Bell Ford. Mixed-load run to **Merecross**. Two wagons, one maintenance cart. Carrow to Bell Ford first day. Bell Ford to Merecross second. Return third if inspection stays clean."

There.

Return third.

Two nights.

Simple.

Probably.

"Why me?"

"Passenger trial needs one mobility rider who used west approach before failure and reopening inspection. That's you."

"Again?"

"Different section. Bell Ford west span into old river road. Also Merecross transit office asked for somebody who can describe Carrow house-access practices because they're changing public gallery rules."

I stared.

"I'm not public-access specialist."

"I told them."

"And?"

"They said good. They want user and worker perspective, not specialist certification."

Specific.

Better.

"Paid?"

"Travel, meals, beds covered. Four copper for two travel days plus Merecross meeting. Return day not paid unless delayed by road work."

Four copper.

Not enormous.

But no travel cost.

And Merecross.

I had never been.

Second life had become embarrassingly local.

First life had crossed kingdoms because somebody pointed at map and said problem.

Now Bell Ford had felt exotic because roof tiles were blue.

I looked paper.

"What does delayed mean?"

"If road closes under inspection, transit office covers lodging until route clears or sends alternate return. One copper per extra duty day if they keep you for inspection interviews or access checks. No pay if you choose to stay for fun."

"Can I leave independently?"

"From Merecross? Sure. If you can pay your own route."

"How much?"

"Depends route. Passenger wagon back to Bell Ford usually three copper. Bell Ford to Carrow one or two depending seat and load."

Potential five copper home.

I had eleven.

Not trapped.

But not nothing.

"Who else?"

"Me. Two road crew. Driver Harl. Bell Ford inspector Rell Dask."

Rell.

"Sun-gnat Rell?"

Sella blinked.

"What?"

"Never mind."

Names remained hostile.

"And one Merecross clerk riding from Bell Ford."

"Weapons?"

Sella looked at crutches.

"You asking what you can carry or whether road dangerous?"

"Both."

"Normal road. Patrol route. No special threat notice. Carry legal personal gear. Maintenance wagons aren't escort caravan."

Good.

No manufactured quest.

"What exactly do I bring?"

"Clothes. Rain layer. Whatever you need for leg. Papers. Don't bring whole house."

Tavi said, "Bring dark lamp."

Sella looked at him.

"What dark lamp?"

"Nothing," I said.

Tavi betrayed me with enthusiasm.

"Greg has lamp that makes light less."

Sella looked at me.

"Three Reeds shade unit."

Recognition.

Not surprise.

"Why would you bring that?"

"I wasn't."

"Good."

Everybody had become Hessa.

Cala read paper.

Longer than I expected.

"Beds where?"

"Bell Ford transit house first night. Merecross south public house second. Shared room possible, mobility-access ground room requested for Greg."

"Requested or confirmed?"

Cala asked.

Sella checked bottom.

"Bell Ford confirmed. Merecross marked requested."

Cala pointed.

"Confirm before he leaves."

Sella nodded.

No defensiveness.

Good.

"Food?"

"Two meals each travel day, evening meal at houses. Personal snacks personal."

"Return?"

"Scheduled third morning."

Cala looked at me.

"You want go."

Not question.

"Yes."

Tavi said, "Can I go?"

Three adults said, "No."

He frowned.

"Why?"

Cala: "School."

Sella: "Work convoy."

Me: "You'd steal bridge."

"Can't steal bridge."

"That's what makes you dangerous."

Cala slid paper back.

"Then go."

That easy.

I had expected resistance.

Maybe because leaving felt bigger to me than everyone else.

Cala had lived whole life with roads existing.

I had too.

But second life had made Carrow small enough to fit in hands.

Leaving meant putting it down.

For three days.

Supposedly.

"Lyssa?" Cala asked.

Right.

Not permission.

Tell people.

Accumulation created notification obligations.

Annoying.

Sella said, "Need answer by noon."

"Yes."

She nodded.

"Be at South Gate before first bell tomorrow. Pack for two nights."

Two nights.

There it was again.

I should have distrusted repetition.

After Sella left, Tavi asked whether Merecross had monsters.

"Probably."

Cala looked at me.

"Normal ones."

"What's normal monster?" Tavi asked.

Complicated question.

"One people know where lives."

He considered.

"Like Naro?"

I laughed hard enough crutch slipped.

Cala did not.

Which made worse.

Packing became artifact problem immediately.

Not because I needed artifact.

Because owning objects meant choosing what stayed when I left.

Chest opened.

Holdfast.

K-14-6 rest stone.

Three Reeds.

Step Keeper.

Oval in bag usually.

I stared.

First impulse: bring all.

Wrong.

Why?

No expected use.

No secure private storage guaranteed beyond transit house.

Weight.

Theft.

Confusion.

Cross-jurisdiction rules maybe.

Also if house burned while gone, chest would burn.

That thought did not help.

I closed chest.

Then opened again.

Cala stood doorway.

"Having fight?"

"Yes."

"Winning?"

"Chest is."

She came in.

"What do you actually need?"

"Nothing magical."

"Then leave it."

"Oval normally stays bag."

"Do you need it?"

No.

But useful enough to carry, not trusted enough to obey.

That had been rule.

Travel was exactly when useful unknown threshold object might tempt me.

Which was argument both directions.

"I don't know."

Cala shrugged.

"Then decide."

Brutal.

I chose oval.

Not because expected use.

Because it had become regular carried equipment and weighed little.

Everything else stayed locked.

Step Keeper especially stayed.

No convenient trip validation.

I wrote card:

TRAVEL: CARROW -> BELL FORD -> MERECROSS
EXPECTED 2 NIGHTS
OVAL IN BAG
HOLDFAST / K-14-6 / THREE REEDS / STEP KEEPER LOCKED HOME
CALA HAS HOUSE KEY, NOT ARTIFACT INSTRUCTION
DO NOT OPEN CHEST FOR PEOPLE

Cala read last.

"I know."

"It's for me."

"You're leaving."

"Then it's for future argument."

She left.

Clothes easier.

Two shirts.

One spare trouser.

Underclothes.

Rain wrap.

Sock for right foot.

Residual-limb cloths.

Skin salve.

Spare wrap.

Small needle/thread.

Crutch tip spare.

Leather strap.

Water bottle.

Notebook.

Papers.

Porter harness?

Yes.

Mundane.

Right-side carry geometry already proven under light load.

I checked replacement buckle warning.

Still not trusted over ten or twelve pounds until pin changed.

Pack stayed under.

No scale.

Estimated.

I distributed heavier cloth in shoulder bag, light roll harness side.

Travel with crutches made luggage personal enemy.

First life pack had weighed more than Tavi.

First life also had two legs.

Details.

Lyssa arrived before noon with heat-cloth sample wrapped separately.

"Cala says you're leaving."

Tone.

Not angry.

Not happy.

Something.

"Three days."

"She said two nights."

"Same thing."

"Not if you leave before first bell and come back after dark."

Fair.

"Work convoy to Merecross."

She stopped.

"Merecross?"

"You've been?"

"Once."

Of course.

"Why?"

"My aunt lived there for half year. I was fourteen."

"What's it like?"

She sat.

"Windy. Too many stairs. River smells sweet near dye quarter and terrible near tanner lane. Market is big enough to lose somebody in. People think their bridge is important."

"Is it?"

"Very. That's why annoying."

Useful.

"Anything I should know?"

"Don't buy food from first row off south gate."

"Why?"

"Tourist prices."

"I'm traveler, not tourist."

"You bought half bridge for six copper."

Cruel.

"Unrelated."

"Very related."

She noticed packed roll.

Face changed.

"You're actually going."

There.

Not concept.

Bag.

"Yes."

She touched edge of rain wrap.

"Three days?"

"Scheduled."

I should not have added word.

She heard it.

"Scheduled."

"Road inspection. Could delay if they close section. Transit office covers lodging if that happens."

"How long?"

"Unknown."

"Greg."

"Probably not long."

"That's not information."

She was right.

I hated when everybody right on same day.

"I don't know. Plan says home third day."

Lyssa looked at me.

"Okay."

Not okay bad.

Okay processing.

"I can not go."

She frowned.

"Why would you not go?"

"Because you look..."

Danger.

"Finish sentence carefully."

"Like you have opinion."

"I always have opinion."

"True."

She leaned back.

"Go. I don't want you sitting here because I looked weird for ten seconds."

"You looked weird twelve."

"Go farther."

I smiled.

Then she said, quieter, "Just don't disappear."

That was different.

First life memory moved before I wanted.

People saying see you after winter.

People I did not see.

Roads closing.

Orders changing.

War.

Monster migration.

One stupid fever.

A bridge gone.

A person gone.

I said, "I'll send word if route changes."

Not promise impossible return.

Promise information.

She nodded.

"Good."

Hessa good?

No.

Not joke.

I let it sit.

Then she handed me heat-cloth sample.

I recoiled.

"Why?"

"Look. Not touch."

She unfolded enough.

Seams tighter than normal service cloth. Edge fold doubled differently. Black orientation thread remained at selvage corner.

"Marra accepted stitch sample this morning," Lyssa said. "Not whole cloth yet. She checked seam spacing and fold direction. Said finish full sample and bring tomorrow."

"You'll be working while I'm gone."

"Yes, Greg. Time continues."

Rude.

Important.

Home did not freeze when protagonist left.

I did not write that because horrifyingly editorial thought.

Instead: "Good."

She pointed.

"Hessa."

"Damn it."

That evening Nessa runner came with theatre call for two days later.

First independent house-cover shift.

Two copper.

I would be in Merecross.

There.

Cost.

Not huge.

Real.

"Decline," I said.

Runner marked slate.

"Reason?"

"Out of city for road work."

"Expected back?"

"Third day."

"Want next available after?"

I paused.

"Yes."

Runner marked.

Opportunity did not vanish because I missed one.

Maybe.

But I could not do everything.

Good.

No.

Annoying.

Vale heard somehow before I told him.

He sent note:

MERECROSS HAS VALE OFFICE.
DO NOT PAY THEM ANYTHING ON MY ACCOUNT.
THEY ARE NOT AUTHORIZED TO COLLECT YOUR CARROW BALANCE.
IF SOMEONE CLAIMS OTHERWISE, GET NAME AND PAPER.

Under it:

IF YOU SEE NORTH RIVER DISPOSAL BOARD, COPY PUBLIC LOT DATES.
NO PAYMENT. CURIOSITY ONLY.

I laughed.

Then wrote back:

YOUR CURIOSITY HAS EMPLOYMENT PROBLEM.

No answer.

Morning came too early.

Cala packed food despite convoy meals.

"For road."

"Food included."

"Road doesn't care."

Fine.

Tavi gave me rock.

Bell Ford striped rock.

"No."

"Bring it back."

"It came from there."

"Exactly."

Child logic impossible.

"Why?"

"So it visits."

I stared.

Rock weighed maybe half pound.

My pack already annoying.

Tavi looked hopeful.

I put rock in shoulder bag.

Terrible precedent.

"If this becomes mountain, no."

"Okay."

Cala hugged me.

Brief.

Normal.

Then checked harness buckle herself.

"You said this one not heavy."

"Under ten-ish pounds."

"I don't like ish."

"Neither does leatherworker."

"Then don't make it heavy."

"Yes."

Lyssa wasn't there.

She had said goodbye previous night.

Fine.

I told myself fine until South Gate.

She was waiting South Gate.

Charcoal coat.

Hair still wet.

Annoying woman.

"You didn't need come."

"I know."

Sella pretended not listen while checking wagon lashings.

Lyssa handed small wrapped packet.

"Bread. Not treated weave."

"Good clarification."

"Cala said road doesn't care."

Conspiracy.

I took it.

"Thank you."

She looked at wagon.

"Two nights."

"Scheduled."

"Stop saying scheduled."

"Sorry."

"Send word if not."

"I will."

She hugged me.

Longer than Cala.

Not sexual.

Worse.

Made leaving actual.

Then stepped back.

"Go buy another useless thing."

"That's support?"

"Within budget."

"You've changed."

"Go."

I went.

South Gate opened.

Carrow road beyond was familiar first mile.

I had walked pieces.

Worked pieces.

Been passenger.

Today wagon seat had my name on transit slate.

Not literally.

Probably.

Sella climbed maintenance cart.

Driver Harl was thick-necked older woman with white braid and habit of speaking to horses as coworkers.

"Left mare's Pella," she told me. "Right is Dorn. Dorn thinks puddles are assassination attempts. If he jumps, don't blame road."

Different speech.

"Does Pella have flaw?"

"Judgment. She chose Dorn."

Harl clicked tongue.

Wagon moved.

Carrow receded.

No fanfare.

Gate smaller.

Rooflines lower behind wall.

Then bend took them.

I felt something stupid in chest.

Not fear.

Not exactly.

First life had left places constantly.

Second life had taught me staying.

Apparently staying made leaving harder.

Also better.

Road inspection itself stayed boring for hours.

Good.

We stopped at marker posts.

Crew checked seams.

I got down twice, walked short sections, reported crutch tip slip on one wet stone shoulder and no issue on another.

Nobody discovered ancient evil.

Dorn survived puddles.

At midday we ate under road shelter.

Sella asked, "Regret?"

"Leaving?"

"Coming."

"No."

"Good."

Everybody.

"If you say that like Hessa, I'm walking home."

Sella looked confused.

"What?"

"Nothing."

Bell Ford appeared late afternoon with bells before roofs.

Wind moved sound down valley.

Different from first visit because now I knew what was coming.

Recognition.

Dangerous cousin of home.

Transit house had ground room confirmed.

Cala would approve.

Rell Dask met us there.

Not sun-gnat Rell.

Man with shaved head and three pens tied to belt.

He talked fast, interrupted himself, and apologized to nobody.

"You're Carrow rider. Good. Tomorrow west span, old river road, then Merecross south gate by late bell if nothing behaves creatively. If something behaves creatively, we stop. We do not negotiate with roads. Roads win by mass."

I liked him.

"What counts creative?"

"Movement where no movement listed. Sound where no sound listed. Heat. Cold. Blue light. Green light. White light unless sun. Animals refusing surface. People reporting same body sensation independently. One person says weird, we note. Two unrelated say same weird, we stop and look."

That was excellent procedure.

"Any expected issue?"

"No. If expected, not trial."

Also excellent.

Dinner stew.

Bed narrow.

Crutches against wall.

First night away from Carrow in longer than I wanted calculate.

I expected sleep difficult.

It wasn't.

That offended me.

Morning west span began with rain.

Dorn considered assassination.

Harl argued him through.

"It's water, you upholstered idiot. Sky has done this before."

Dorn disagreed physically.

Eventually road won.

We crossed Bell Ford bridge.

Bells overhead and below.

Tavi's rock in bag returning to homeland.

I did not tell anyone.

Old river road climbed west.

New country.

Not dramatically.

Trees changed first.

Tall pale trunks with red moss on north sides.

Then roadside drainage changed from stone mouths to narrow copper-lined cuts.

Then houses spaced farther.

Then fields with low silver flags turning in wind.

I asked Rell.

"Bird markers?"

"Windmidge breaks."

"What are windmidges?"

He looked at me.

"Carrow doesn't get them?"

"Apparently not."

Finally.

Something I genuinely did not know because local.

"Tiny flying bastards. Follow pressure seams. Get in grain vents. Flags disrupt line enough they spread instead of pile. Don't ask me biology. Ask farmer."

Good.

World expanded by insect nuisance.

Perfect.

At second road stop, maintenance crew found first problem.

Not disaster.

A marker nail had risen half finger from timber edge.

Rell stopped entire convoy.

Measured.

Compared paper.

Crew reseated after checking timber sound.

Fifteen minutes.

Then onward.

By midday Merecross road signs appeared.

MERECROSS 8

Then 6.

Then 4.

Distance units local enough I understood from road context, not worth lecture.

At three, road changed.

Not physically.

Traffic.

Carts coming east.

More than expected.

First three ordinary.

Fourth loaded household furniture.

Fifth sacks and children.

Sixth empty merchant wagon moving fast.

Rell raised hand.

Our convoy slowed.

Another cart passed.

Driver shouted something.

Harl shouted back.

I caught only:

"South span closed!"

Rell stood in cart.

"Which span?"

Driver already past.

Sella swore.

Not panic.

Information changed.

At next roadside post, a transit runner waited.

Red-white sash.

Mud to knees.

He waved us down.

"Merecross South River span shut since morning second bell. East foot still open. Wagons diverted north ford."

Rell asked, "Cause?"

"Pier response."

That phrase did not mean enough.

"What response?"

"Downstream pier started counting wrong."

Silence.

There.

Fantasy infrastructure.

Not ours.

Not chest.

Not Carrow.

Rell said, "Define counting."

Runner breathed.

"Bridge keeper says load stones are returning one extra pulse after heavy crossing. Started intermittent. Now every third or fourth wagon. Engineers closed vehicle deck. Foot deck remains under restricted flow because foot loads aren't producing extra return."

I leaned forward.

Old systems.

Load stones.

Counting.

Merecross.

My Step Keeper at home had documented old function passive edge count / return indication.

No.

Do not connect because words overlap.

Different object.

Different system.

Different city.

But curiosity woke violently.

Rell asked, "North ford passable?"

"Yes, but queue half day."

"Our maintenance wagons?"

"Priority only if Merecross office confirms inspection status."

"Where's clerk?"

Merecross clerk riding from Bell Ford raised hand from second wagon.

Young man named Pavin who had spoken maybe six sentences all day.

Now he became important.

"I can authorize entry priority," Pavin said. "Not return."

Return.

There it was.

Rell looked at road paper.

"We can reach city north by dark if priority."

Sella asked runner, "South span expected reopen tomorrow?"

Runner laughed once.

Not humor.

"No estimate."

Two-night trip cracked.

Not broken yet.

Alternate route existed.

North ford.

Half-day queue.

Could still return third day maybe late.

Rell looked at me.

"Passenger contract says delay lodging covered. You can turn back now with eastbound carts if you want."

Choice.

Actual.

Carrow behind.

Merecross ahead with bridge whose load stones were counting wrong.

I had eleven copper.

Work obligation technically Merecross meeting.

Lyssa said don't disappear.

I had promised word if route changed.

Not promised avoid change.

"Can we send message from city?"

Pavin said, "Transit office dispatches east every morning. Emergency notes sooner if route runner."

"Then I'm continuing."

Rell nodded.

No congratulations.

Good.

North ford route added four hours.

Merecross appeared after sunset.

Not city like Carrow.

Larger.

Much larger.

River split it.

Lights on both banks.

Three bridges visible from north rise.

One dark.

South span.

Even from distance I saw lamps along vehicle deck extinguished, foot deck marked by narrow blue-white guide lights.

Center bridge arched high with towers.

North bridge low and broad.

And beyond, market roofs layered uphill until lost in dark.

Wind carried smell Lyssa remembered.

Sweet first.

Then leather rot.

"Dye quarter," I said.

Pavin looked at me.

"You know Merecross?"

"Someone described smell."

"Accurate."

We entered through north gate because south approach closed.

Transit house reservation had been south.

Of course.

Pavin checked slate at gate office.

Face tightened.

"South public house is across closed wagon span. Foot crossing possible, but your ground room got reassigned after our delay notice because they thought convoy diverted overnight."

There.

Bed gone.

"Alternative?" Sella asked.

"North transit annex has bunks upstairs."

Everyone looked at me.

Crutches.

Stairs.

I could climb stairs.

Slowly.

But luggage.

And after full travel day.

"How many?"

"Twenty-two steps, landing, twelve."

Specific.

"Rail?"

"Both sides first flight, left only second."

I considered.

"I can do it. Bag carried separately."

Sella said, "You don't have to prove anything."

"I'm not. I want bed."
