Pavin said, "There may be inn rooms north market, but transit office only covers contracted lodging rate. Difference personal unless accessibility failure is ours."

"Reservation lost because your delay notice?"

Pavin paused.

"Yes."

"Then accessibility failure is yours."

He stared.

Sella looked away, maybe hiding smile.

Pavin said, "That's fair."

Good clerk.

"I can authorize equivalent ground room up to four copper tonight."

Four copper lodging.

Bigger city.

Bigger prices.

"Find one?"

"We can try."

Try became forty minutes.

North market inns full because south-span closure stranded merchants.

First: no ground rooms.

Second: one ground room but doorway too narrow for cargo, irrelevant to me, and bed available. Five copper.

Transit covers four.

I would owe one.

Could pay.

Third farther uphill reportedly four copper, but another six streets.

I had traveled all day.

One copper now meant convenience, not crisis.

"I'll pay difference," I said.

Pavin documented.

One copper.

Eleven to ten.

No panic.

Room small.

Ground floor.

Bed.

Wash basin.

Window facing alley.

Worth one copper personal difference tonight.

I sent note through transit office before eating:

CALA / LYSSA

IN MERECROSS. SOUTH RIVER WAGON SPAN CLOSED. WE ENTERED NORTH. RETURN MAY DELAY. I AM FINE. TRANSIT COVERING WORK DELAY. WILL SEND AGAIN WHEN ROUTE KNOWN.

GREG

I added:

TAVI: ROCK VISITED BELL FORD.

Important diplomacy.

Dispatch east at dawn.

Would reach Bell Ford, then Carrow maybe tomorrow evening if routes cooperated.

Information had travel time.

Promise fulfilled as well as possible.

Dinner at inn cost included in transit meal chit up to listed bowl.

I ate stew.

Different stew.

More pepper.

Adventure.

I almost laughed.

Then somebody at next table said, "They're closing foot deck too."

I looked.

Merchant answered, "Who said?"

"My cousin's boy."

Bad source.

I kept eating.

Five minutes later bell sounded outside.

Three low.

Pause.

Three low.

People in room stopped.

Innkeeper swore.

"What?" I asked.

He looked at me like foreigner.

I was.

"River closure. Full span."

There.

Not rumor now.

I stood.

Crutches.

Outside, people moved toward river despite closure because people loved seeing thing they should avoid.

I did not.

Growth.

Instead went transit annex.

Pavin was already there.

Rell too.

Sella.

Road crew.

Map open.

Pavin looked at me.

"South span foot deck closed. Extra return appeared under pedestrian group. No crossing now."

"North ford?"

"Open tonight. River rising from upper rain. If it rises another hand, wagons close."

"Center bridge?"

"City traffic only under reduced load. No outbound maintenance convoy until engineers clear."

"Tomorrow return?"

Pavin exhaled.

"Unlikely."

Two nights died officially.

"How long?"

"No estimate."

Sella said, "Carrow can manage without us."

I knew.

That was not problem.

Problem was I suddenly could not put date on home.

Not trapped exactly.

Roads existed north and west.

Money existed.

But contracted return route did not.

Merecross office meeting still tomorrow.

Bridge problem now larger than meeting.

Rell pointed map.

"Transit wants our trial convoy held. We're useful witnesses on west road condition and mixed-load run. They may ask crew to help reroute inspection traffic."

Pavin looked at me.

"Your passenger/access meeting still stands, but after that you are not obligated unless you accept additional duty. Lodging remains covered while return is unavailable under contract."

Good.

Boundaries.

"Can I independently leave north?"

"Yes. North road to Alder Basin. From there east routes eventually reconnect. Four to six days depending seats. Your cost unless transit authorizes alternate return."

Four to six.

There was adventure hiding behind bureaucracy.

I looked at map.

Merecross larger than Carrow.

Alder Basin north.

River towns west.

South road currently cut.

Bell Ford east behind us.

World suddenly had directions again.

First life feeling.

Not nostalgia exactly.

Scale.

I had forgotten scale.

"What happens if span stays closed?"

Pavin said, "Then city reroutes until repaired."

"How long can repair take?"

"Depends what's wrong."

Rell said, "Could be tomorrow. Could be month."

Month.

Nobody dramatic.

Just possibility.

I thought of Cala's kitchen.

Tavi's rock.

Lyssa's wet hair at gate.

Theatre call already missed.

Chest locked.

Vale twenty-three copper.

Eleven now ten.

Home existed.

That was what made map interesting instead of empty.

Pavin said, "We reconvene first bell."

I nodded.

Back at inn I opened notebook.

CASH: 10 C
VALE: 23 C
MERECROSS LODGING DIFFERENCE: 1 C
RETURN: DELAYED / UNKNOWN

Then:

SOUTH SPAN
- LOAD-STONE EXTRA RETURN UNDER HEAVY WAGON CROSSING
- LATER REPORTED UNDER PEDESTRIAN GROUP
- FULL CLOSURE
- CAUSE UNKNOWN
- DO NOT CONNECT TO STEP KEEPER / K-14 / ANYTHING ELSE BY WORD SIMILARITY

Then travel:

CARROW -> BELL FORD -> MERECROSS
EXPECTED 2 NIGHTS
NO LONGER TRUE

I stared at last line.

Crossed out `EXPECTED 2 NIGHTS`.

Wrote:

HOME DATE UNKNOWN.

That should have scared me more.

It did scare me.

A little.

Good.

Not Hessa good.

Alive good.

I closed notebook.

Outside, closure bells sounded again across river.

Merecross did not know me.

Nobody here cared about Greg's usual chair, Greg's lamp habits, Greg's debt, Greg's theatre call list, Greg's chest, Greg's reputation.

Excellent.

Terrible.

I had come as passenger representative for two nights.

Now I had a room, ten copper, a road problem, a city I did not know, and no date home.

Delegate.

For what, exactly, tomorrow could decide.

# CHAPTER 381

## THE VISITOR

Merecross charged for stairs.

Not officially.

It just built enough of them that eventually somebody paid.

Usually me.

First bell found transit office uphill from inn.

Naturally.

I arrived damp, irritated, and seven minutes early because spite remained effective mobility aid.

Pavin was already there.

Rell too.

Sella had tea.

I had betrayal.

"Where?" I asked.

She pointed corner urn.

Free.

Merecross forgiven temporarily.

Transit office occupied old stone building with three public counters, two private rooms, one wall map large enough to invade countries, and constant movement around South River closure.

Clerks carried red-white boards between desks.

Runners came wet through side door, shouted route conditions, received paper, left again.

Nobody looked calm.

Nobody looked panicked either.

City had practiced inconvenience.

Pavin gave us morning status.

"South span remains full closed. North ford vehicle restriction begins if gauge reaches black nail. It's half finger below. Center bridge reduced-load city permits only. Our return convoy held through noon minimum."

Rell asked, "Any repeated extra return overnight?"

"No load tests overnight. Engineers stopped testing after pedestrian response."

Good.

Not endlessly poking dangerous thing because curious.

I drank tea.

Pavin continued.

"Your access meeting moved from South Office to North Civic Hall because south staff cannot cross."

"Can I?"

"Yes."

"Ground floor?"

"Mostly."

Mostly was dangerous architecture word.

"Define."

"Meeting room ground. Public gallery has seven-step demonstration rise. They want you to look, not necessarily climb."

"Fine."

Sella said, "I'm going with road crew to north ford."

Rell: "I'm going South span observation post. No deck access."

Pavin: "I have closure desk until noon."

Everybody had job.

I had meeting.

Then maybe nothing.

Excellent.

I liked nothing in foreign city.

Pavin handed me temporary transit paper.

VISITING ACCESS DELEGATE
CARROW / BELL FORD TRIAL PARTY
NORTH CIVIC HALL

Delegate persisted.

"This gets me what?"

"Through closure cordons relevant to meeting. Not bridge cordon. Not work yards. Not storage. Not free lunch."

"Cruel."

"Civic Hall has lunch."

"Then paper stronger than stated."

Pavin almost smiled.

New people required calibration.

Good.

North Civic Hall sat four streets higher.

Merecross hated flat ground because river had apparently used all of it.

City climbed both banks in terraces, stairs, ramps, alleys, bridges between buildings, and narrow freight lifts that looked magical until I saw chain housings.

Some were magical too.

Could not assume.

First market row sold rope, lamp oil, travel bread, cheap rain caps, copper tags, bridge charms, and tiny painted models of center bridge.

Tourist prices maybe.

Lyssa warning applied south gate, not here.

I did not buy bridge.

Growth.

Second row sold tools.

Danger.

I kept moving.

Civic Hall had broad front stair and side ramp.

Ramp longer but reasonable.

Door guard looked at paper, looked at crutches, then looked back at paper.

Good order.

"Room six. Left corridor."

No grabbing.

Carrow had trained standards into me apparently.

Room six contained four people and a wooden model of public gallery.

Not building model.

Seating section.

Steps, rail, removable benches, aisle markers.

Woman at head table stood.

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"Iven Sar. Civic access records. Thank you for still coming despite closure."

Iven was maybe forty, hair braided tight against head, sleeves ink-stained. Speech careful, almost legal.

Others:

Bera Noll, gallery superintendent, large man with formal vest and expression of person whose chairs personally disappointed him.

Kett, carpenter responsible for public fittings, missing two fingertips on right hand and speaking in long paragraphs before anybody asked.

And Ossa Vey, who introduced herself as "complaints desk, unfortunately."

Not dry.

Exhausted.

Good.

Iven pointed chair.

"This is not certification."

"Good."

"We have specialist review next month. Closure has delayed one reviewer. We asked Carrow for user/work perspective because your West House recently documented rail-grip complaint and because transit records show you use crutches."

Information traveled faster institutionally than personally.

Interesting.

"I can tell you what works for me and what I saw patrons choose. That's it."

"Exactly."

Bera said, "And what Carrow does operationally."

"What I have personally been taught. Not all Carrow."

He nodded, slightly less happy.

Boundaries annoyed people who wanted answers.

Meeting began.

Model first.

Merecross planned replacing fixed back-row benches with removable sections for festival crowd changes.

Question was whether widening one aisle but adding removable seating created better access.

My answer: depends who needs what and whether staff actually preserve aisle when crowded.

Not useful enough.

So I explained West House rules I knew.

Capacity marks count bodies, not occupied seats.

No standing behind back rail.

Bags and stools stay out aisle.

Ask mobility-aid user what support they want.

Hold flow if slower stair chosen.

Offer floor alternative.

Do not release dark gallery until lighting confirmed.

Ossa wrote quickly.

Bera asked, "Do people object when you hold flow?"

"Yes."

"How do you manage?"

"They remain alive while annoyed."

Bera blinked.

Ossa laughed into paper.

Greg register survived border.

"More seriously," I said, "house lead backs rule. If door worker has to negotiate every time, rule isn't rule."

Iven wrote that.

Kett had rail question.

Of course.

"We have ornamental turn joint at demonstration rise. Complaint says palm closure becomes incomplete at carved swell, but swell is integral to splice cover, and if we shave it we expose fasteners, and if we change splice cover we're not changing rail, we're changing joint family, which means drawings, which means procurement, which means somebody will tell me I should have known before installation even though drawing was approved by people with hands."

He inhaled.

Speech variance achieved naturally by man furious at drawings.

"Can I see it?"

We went gallery.

Seven steps.

I climbed because wanted actual geometry.

Slow.

No performance.

Rail on both sides.

At turn joint, right rail widened dramatically into carved square flower.

I tried grip.

My hand could not close.

Could still palm top.

Different.

"For me, worse," I said. "But I'm not cane user from Carrow. Her complaint was hand couldn't close fully around thick turn. This does same thing."

Kett said, "Yes."

Bera: "But only one step."

I looked at him.

"Falls only need one."

He exhaled.

"I know."

Good.

Not villain.

Iven asked, "Would contrasting edge mark help?"

"Maybe visibility. Doesn't make rail thinner."

"Would second lower rail?"

"Ask specialist and users. I don't know."

Ossa said, "Can we stop trying to make visitor solve carpentry?"

I liked Ossa.

We moved on.

The useful thing was not my answers.

It was complaints desk.

Ossa showed ledger categories.

Merecross logged complaints by building, fixture, date, injury/no injury, repeated location, and whether staff action occurred.

Carrow West House had apparently failed at this because Maro heard rail complaint before but didn't convert it into record.

Merecross system better.

Except Ossa pointed at stack.

"System works if anyone reads it."

There were six months of flagged repeats awaiting quarterly review.

Ah.

Every city same in new dialect.

"How many rail complaints?"

"This gallery? Eleven in two years. Three at turn joint. Two mention grip. One fall, no injury."

Kett stared.

"I was told one."

Ossa's face changed.

Not triumphant.

Tired.

"You were sent maintenance requests. Complaints aren't automatically maintenance unless coded."

Iven closed eyes.

There.

Not evil.

Routing failure.

My new profession haunted me.

"Who codes?" I asked.

Ossa: "Desk. But grip complaints were coded access, not maintenance."

"Who reads access?"

"Quarterly review."

"When next?"

"Next month."

Kett said something impolite about months.

Bera said, "Can we issue temporary wrap?"

Kett immediately: "If approved material, yes, but don't wrap decorative joint with soft rope because it changes hand diameter and can rotate, and if somebody tells you cloth I will leave building."

Nobody said cloth.

He continued anyway.

"Leather sleeve with flat seam maybe, but still diameter. Better temporary bypass grip fixed below if attachment rated. I need inspect underside."

There.

Work.

Meeting changed because actual ledger met actual carpenter.

Not because Greg solved.

Iven looked at me.

"This is useful."

"Because I asked who reads column?"

"Sometimes yes."

Vale would love this city.

Terrifying.

Lunch was bread, lentils, cheese.

Free enough.

During lunch, closure bell sounded once.

Not full pattern.

Everyone paused.

Iven explained.

"North ford vehicle closure."

There.

River reached black nail.

Return route narrowed again.

Nobody ran.

Merecross absorbed.

Bera said, "That'll wreck festival freight."

Festival.

First mention.

"What festival?"

Four people looked at me.

Foreigner again.

Ossa said, "Glasswake."

Nothing.

Kett leaned forward, delighted to explain.

Oh no.

"River glass. Not glass glass, though there's glass. Every autumn first cold current comes down from north basin and wakes the old mirror beds under west channel, so at night the water throws light upward instead of reflecting down, except technically it's refractive return through suspended mineral sheets, unless the priests are speaking, in which case river remembers stars, and you should not correct them because they control lantern permits."

I loved Merecross.

"When?"

"Six days."

Ah.

Bridge closure six days before major festival.

Now economics.

Bera groaned.

"Hotels already full."

Ossa said, "Which is why your one-copper room difference was miracle."

She knew.

"How?"

"Pavin called asking ground rooms."

City network.

Again.

"Does closure cancel festival?"

Iven shook head.

"Not yet. Glasswake happens whether city is ready. Public events can be reduced. River doesn't care."

There.

Fantasy event independent of governance.

"Dangerous?"

Kett started.

Iven raised hand.

"Normal Glasswake is managed. Strong glare on west water, some navigation restrictions, mirror-bed licensing, crowd control. Ask river office for mechanism."

Boundaries.

Excellent.

Ossa said, "The dangerous part is drunk people leaning over railings."

Bera looked personally attacked.

"We manage railings."

"You manage people near railings. Different problem."

Lunch ended.

Meeting contract technically done.

Iven signed my transit paper.

"Your four-copper travel and meeting payment can be released at transit office this afternoon."

Good.

Ten to fourteen once collected.

Not yet.

"Anything else?"

Iven hesitated.

"Possibly."

There it was.

Adventure loved possibly.

"Closure has stranded one of our outside access reviewers south of river. Center bridge permits might move her tomorrow, but uncertain. We need temporary user walkthroughs of three Glasswake public sites before festival setup locks. Not certification. Recorded observations only."

"Why me?"

"Because you're here, you use crutches, and you already understand difference between observation and certification."

"How many sites?"

"Three. North market viewing terrace, West Steps river platform, old ferry hall."

"Stairs?"

"Many."

Of course.

"Pay?"

"Two copper per site, transit provided within city where available. One site per half day."

Six copper possible.

Real work.

Not charity.

"Do you have local mobility users?"

Iven looked pleased.

Correct question.

"Yes. We would pair you. Different users, different observations."

"Then why need me?"

"Because festival committee requested outside perspective after last year's crowd plan passed local review and still failed visiting cane users at West Steps. Familiarity hides things."

Reasonable.

"I can do one tomorrow. Then decide."

"Accepted."

"Which?"

"North market terrace. Least complicated."

That sounded like lie.

Nobody meant it as lie.

Danger.

Iven wrote authorization.

"Your local partner is **Dema Orr**. She uses forearm cane, works market licensing, and will meet you at second bell."

"Does she know I'm not specialist?"

"Yes."

"Does she know I'm being paid?"

Iven blinked.

Mara rule.

"She will be paid same two copper for walkthrough."

Good.

"Then yes."

Ossa looked at me.

"You negotiate weird."

"Recently learned incentives exist."

"Terrible discovery."

Exactly.

Outside Civic Hall, city had changed while we talked.

North ford closure pushed carts into streets around center bridge permit queue.

Merchants shouting.

Porters redirecting.

Public boards updated with chalk:

SOUTH SPAN CLOSED ALL TRAFFIC
NORTH FORD CLOSED VEHICLES / FOOT OPEN
CENTER BRIDGE CITY PERMIT LOADS ONLY
EASTBOUND THROUGH FREIGHT HOLD

Through freight hold.

Home farther by chalk.

I should have gone transit office for payment.

I did.

Discipline.

Pavin was at counter now with sleeves rolled.

"Meeting complete?"

"Yes."

He checked signature.

Paid four copper.

Fourteen.

Actual.

"Return?"

"No outbound convoy tomorrow. Earliest reassessment following morning."

"North foot?"

"Open. Passenger wagons no."

"Independent route?"

"You could walk north road."

I looked at him.

He looked at crutches.

"That was stupid sentence."

"Yes."

"Sorry."

Good.

"Any passenger carts beyond ford?"

"Some, but you'd need cross foot, find seat north side, then Alder Basin. Expensive now. Maybe six to eight copper just to Basin because demand."

Prices moved under disruption.

Rational.

"Transit alternate return authorization?"

"Not yet. You're covered here until contracted route available or office authorizes alternative."

So leaving independently now could cost more than half my cash and abandon covered lodging.

No reason.

Also Glasswake.

Also six-copper possible work.

Also city.

I stayed.

Not trapped.

Choice mattered more.

"I accepted one Civic Hall walkthrough tomorrow."

Pavin nodded.

"Then you're local duty?"

"Separate Civic contract."

"Good. Keep papers separate."

Vale had infected world.

"Any message east?"

"Morning dispatch left before north ford vehicle closure. Your note went. Next east runner likely tonight on foot to Bell Ford if weather holds."

I wrote second note.

CALA / LYSSA

RETURN ROUTE STILL CLOSED. NORTH FORD NOW CLOSED TO VEHICLES. I AM STAYING IN MERECROSS UNDER TRANSIT LODGING WHILE ROUTE UNAVAILABLE. I TOOK ONE PAID CIVIC WALKTHROUGH TOMORROW. I AM NOT STRANDED WITHOUT OPTIONS. I HAVE MONEY. I AM FINE.

Then:

MERECROSS HAS FESTIVAL IN SIX DAYS CALLED GLASSWAKE. RIVER APPARENTLY DOES SOMETHING RUDE WITH LIGHT.

Then Tavi:

DO NOT ASK CALA IF YOU CAN COME.

I folded.

Pavin read recipient names only.

"Tonight runner if weather holds."

Good.

Afternoon free.

Actual free.

Foreign city.

Fourteen copper.

I went market.

Of course.

Not to buy.

To look.

This was lie I meant.

North market was larger than entire South Quay recovery sale.

Covered lanes.

Permanent shops.

Temporary tables.

Repair stalls.

Food.

Cloth.

Tools.

River permits.

Festival lantern frames.

Cheap charms shaped like fish.

Real magical goods mixed with mundane enough that signage mattered.

One lane had green placards:

REGISTERED ACTIVE GOODS

Another yellow:

RETIRED / PASSIVE / UNCERTIFIED

My feet, singular and otherwise, turned yellow.

Danger.

First stall: old lamp shutters, warming tiles, broken threshold chimes, two sealed boxes marked municipal surplus.

Prices 6c, 9c, 14c, 3c.

Carrow numbers but wider selection.

Second stall had object I wanted immediately.

Bad sign.

A flat black metal disk, palm-wide, with three folding brass feet and cloudy glass center.

Card:

NORTH CHANNEL NIGHT REST
REGISTERED RETIRED SURVEY AID
DECLARED FUNCTION: LEVEL REFERENCE UNDER MOVING LIGHT
CURRENT RESPONSE: HOLDS HORIZON LINE IN GLASS FOR APPROX. 20 BREATHS AFTER SET
NOT FOR NAVIGATION / LOAD / HEIGHT CERTIFICATION
SERVICE HISTORY PARTIAL
ASK 28 C

Twenty-eight.

Excellent.

Too expensive.

Not absurd.

Seller saw stare.

"Works."

"Card says current response."

"Same thing to buyers."

"Not same."

She smiled.

Older woman, shaved one side hair, gold tooth.

"Good. Twenty-eight."

"Why retired?"

"New survey lamps hold reference without hand-set. This needs flat start, clean glass, and hates blue river nights."

"Hates how?"

"Line wanders during Glasswake."

Useful downside.

"Tested?"

"Last month ordinary night. Want demonstration, one bit after dusk. Applied to price if buy same day."

Bits.

I had unspecified bits, dangerous continuity.

"Copper demonstration?"

"One copper gets three objects demonstrated, no purchase credit."

Market structure.

Interesting.

"What other two?"

She pointed.

A brass fork tagged current-finder, 19c.

A stone-backed clip tagged quiet hinge keeper, 11c.

I wanted all three because disease.

"Not today," I said.

"Glasswake starts six days. Night Rest drops to twenty-two after festival if still here because nobody local wants one that wanders during best week of year."

There.

Price dynamics.

"Or rises because visitors?"

She shrugged.

"Then I lied and made money."

Honest seller.

"Name?"

"Tessa Vorn. Stall forty-two."

"Greg."

"Carrow?"

I looked.

"Accent. Also your transit paper is hanging out."

Less magical.

"Do you know Carrow market?"

"Enough to know you people overpay for river hardware because you don't have river."

I liked her less.

Maybe more.

"Twenty-eight is not happening."

"Then come back with twenty-eight or don't."

No hold pressure.

Good.

I left.

Twenty-eight copper.

Object changed scene grammar potentially.

Portable level reference under moving light.

Not combat.

Not universal.

Could matter in travel, rigging, construction, casting? Maybe.

Do not invent uses before understanding.

I wrote:

TESSA VORN / NORTH MARKET 42
NORTH CHANNEL NIGHT REST
28 C NOW
POSSIBLE 22 C AFTER GLASSWAKE IF UNSOLD, SELLER CLAIM ONLY
CURRENT CARD RESPONSE: HORIZON LINE ~20 BREATHS AFTER SET
REQUIRES FLAT START / CLEAN GLASS
SELLER SAYS WANDERS DURING GLASSWAKE
DEMO TERMS: 1 C / THREE OBJECTS, NO CREDIT
DO NOT WANT JUST BECAUSE EXPENSIVE

Last line necessary.

Then I saw board.

NORTH RIVER DISPOSAL.

Vale curiosity.

Public lot dates.

I copied.

Three upcoming:

Day after Glasswake opening: retired ferry fittings / channel markers / damaged survey housings.

Three days later: municipal lamp stock / bridge maintenance surplus.

Following week: old west-lock service equipment, restricted inspection before sale.

That was not for me.

Probably.

I copied exact board heading and dates for Vale.

No payment.

Curiosity only.

Then something stranger.

Small notice pinned beneath disposal board:

OUTBOUND FIELD CREW
ALDER BASIN -> NORTH GLASS CUT -> WEST LOCKS
TEN TO FOURTEEN DAYS
SEEKING ONE ADDITIONAL RECORD HAND / GENERAL SUPPORT
ROAD + RIVER ROUTE
MEALS / BEDROLL / RETURN PASS PROVIDED
6 C START + 2 C PER FIELD DAY AFTER DAY 3
MOBILITY REQUIREMENTS: UNEVEN GROUND, CART ACCESS MOST ROUTE, SHORT FOOT SECTIONS
ASK AT RIVER SURVEY OFFICE

I read twice.

Not for me.

Probably.

Ten to fourteen days.

Alder Basin.

North Glass Cut.

West Locks.

Return pass.

Field crew.

Record hand/general support.

Uneven ground.

Crutches.

Maybe impossible.

Maybe not.

I did not tear notice.

Did not apply.

Just copied.

That was enough.

Adventure did not need jump immediately.

It needed door.

This one was open.

At inn, I ate included stew again.

Pepper still aggressive.

I counted fourteen copper.

Then wrote:

CURRENT OBLIGATIONS
- CIVIC WALKTHROUGH TOMORROW, 2 C IF COMPLETED
- TRANSIT RETURN COVERED BUT NO DATE
- SEND WORD HOME WHEN ROUTE CHANGES

CURRENT TEMPTATIONS
- NIGHT REST 28 C
- DISPOSAL LOTS AFTER GLASSWAKE
- FIELD CREW 10-14 DAYS, ALDER BASIN / NORTH GLASS CUT / WEST LOCKS

I stared at field crew line.

If I took it, bridge reopening would stop mattering.

I would not be delayed away from home.

I would choose farther.

Different.

Too early to decide.

Good.

Outside, Merecross lit festival frames even with bridge dark.

People climbed ladders.

Merchants argued over frontage.

Closure bells had stopped for now.

River below reflected ordinary lights.

In six days, apparently, it would not.

I had come here because Carrow needed a visitor's perspective somewhere else.

Now Merecross had paid me, offered more work, shown me an object I couldn't afford, and pinned a road north to a public board.

Visitor.

For the moment.

# CHAPTER 382

## THE OUTSIDER

Dema Orr hated ramps.

Not all ramps.

Important distinction.

"This one," she said, striking North Market viewing terrace ramp with cane tip, "was designed by somebody who thinks wheels and legs both enjoy scenic detours."

I looked up.

Ramp began twelve feet from stairs, doubled back twice, passed behind a festival lantern storage cage, then reached terrace entrance approximately six years later.

"It's gentle."

"Yes."

"Wide."

"Yes."

"Rail both sides."

"Yes."

"You hate it."

"Deeply."

Dema was shorter than Lyssa, broader through shoulders, maybe late thirties. Dark hair cut at jaw because wind had apparently offended her personally. Forearm cane on left. Market licensing sash under rain coat.

She walked quickly enough that I had already learned not to assume cane meant my pace.

"Why?"

She pointed with cane.

"Lantern cage gets moved there every Glasswake because storage permit says cage can't block east fire lane. Then vendors stack delivery crates beside cage because ramp landing isn't technically stall frontage. Then people using ramp have one shoulder width through. Every year somebody reports. Every year festival ends. Cage goes away. Problem becomes next year's problem."

There.

Not ramp.

Operations.

"Is cage moving this year?"

"No."

"Because we're here?"

"Because I moved permit yesterday."

I liked Dema.

"Then why walkthrough?"

"To find what I missed."

Better.

Second bell had barely sounded.

North Market already preparing Glasswake.

Lantern frames hung from crosswires. Shopkeepers painted river-facing shutters with pale reflective wash. Temporary food stalls marked floor boundaries in chalk. Porters carried stacked benches uphill while complaining at gravity.

Terrace itself projected from north bank above river.

South span visible downstream, dark and cordoned.

Center bridge crowded under permit control.

North ford farther upstream mostly hidden.

River brown-green under cloud.

No upward light yet.

Ordinary river.

Waiting.

Dema held clipboard.

"Iven said you're from Carrow."

"Yes."

"Never been here before two days ago."

"Correct."

"Good."

Everybody.

I waited.

She frowned.

"What?"

"Nothing. Continue."

"Locals know where not to stand without remembering why. Visitors stand there. I want you to be stupid honestly."

"Finally qualified."

"Don't get proud."

Different rhythm.

We started entrance.

Dema did not ask me to inspect dimensions.

She asked me to enter as if evening crowd already present.

Temporary lane ropes divided entry and exit.

I chose entry.

First problem immediate.

Festival sign hung low enough that my left crutch cuff clipped corner when I turned around a family stopped reading it.

Not dangerous alone.

Crowd compression maybe.

Dema marked.

"Sign can move."

"Probably."

"Don't probably my permit."

"Sign can physically move. Whether committee lets you is your tragedy."

She wrote something longer.

At terrace top, viewing rail had three heights.

Normal adult.

Lower child/seated section.

One open bay where removable chair could fit.

Good.

Then festival vendor had placed demonstration lantern pedestal directly in open bay.

Dema stared.

"That was not there yesterday."

Vendor looked up.

"Permit mark is here."

He pointed chalk square.

Dema checked clipboard.

"Your mark is stall twelve-B. This is access bay."

"Twelve-B was chalked here."

"By who?"

"Market chalk crew."

Dema closed eyes.

I knew that face.

Routing failure had cousins.

She found faded blue access mark under fresh white vendor square.

"Move pedestal."

Vendor bristled.

"I paid river frontage."

"You have river frontage six feet left."

"That puts me behind bell post."

"Yes."

"People won't see lantern."

"Then complain licensing."

"You are licensing."

"Excellent. Saves walk. Move it."

He stared.

Dema stared back.

I remained outsider.

Useful role.

Vendor moved pedestal.

Not happily.

Nobody died.

We continued.

Terrace crowd capacity markers were painted under benches.

I would never have known.

Dema did.

She knew which gate stuck in wet weather, which stair became slick when fried-sugar stall washed pans, which shop owner would quietly extend awning into public lane if rain started.

I knew none.

I saw different things.

At west corner, river-facing mirror panel had been mounted at shoulder height.

Decorative festival piece.

Cloudy silver glass angled toward water.

I stopped.

"What?" Dema asked.

"Where does that throw light?"

"Up. Mostly. During Glasswake."

"Mostly?"

"Depends current."

"People stand here?"

"Yes."

"Then why panel at face height?"

Dema looked at it.

"Because it's pretty."

"That's strong safety case."

She ignored.

"Last year complaints?"

"Glare. Headache. One child cried. No injury."

"Can it rotate?"

"Locked after setup."

"Who sets angle?"

"River lighting crew."

"Based on?"

"Ask them."

Good.

No pretending.

We marked for lighting crew review, not Greg solution.

Then actual scene grammar changed.

A porter shouted from lower market.

People turned.

Not emergency shout exactly.

Angry surprise.

A string of festival lantern frames along lower lane had begun slowly rotating in opposite directions.

No wind.

At least none I felt.

Dema looked river.

"Early."

"What is?"

"Glass pull."

"Festival is six days."

"River doesn't read permit."

She moved fast.

I followed.

Lower lane crowded around frames.

Metal hoops suspended from wire, each holding empty mirror-glass sockets. No lamps installed yet.

Every third frame rotated clockwise.

Others still.

Then one reversed.

Not violent.

Strange.

Market workers were already lowering line.

Good.

Dema grabbed licensing runner.

"Who installed?"

"Venn crew."

"River-light approval?"

"Temporary setup only."

"Any glass in frames?"

"No active glass. Sockets empty."

I looked closer from safe distance.

Not empty.

One frame had a tiny shard caught in lower socket.

Could be ordinary broken glass.

Could be reflective.

I did not touch.

"That one has something."

Runner looked.

Dema: "Lower all."

"All?"

"Yes."

"But only line three moving."

"And I don't know why. Lower all."

Good.

They did.

One shopkeeper complained about blocking frontage.

Dema told him to survive inconvenience.

Not exact words.

Close.

When frame reached ground, river-light worker arrived carrying padded gloves and a black wooden case.

Name **Maro Pell**.

No relation claimed to deceased Pell Arven. Common names existed.

Maro spoke softly and asked everyone to stop guessing.

I liked him instantly.

He inspected shard without touching first.

"Old mirror flake."

Dema asked, "Active?"

"Reactive, maybe. Active isn't useful word here."

Excellent.

"Why in socket?"

"Could have stuck from storage. Could have blown. Could have been put."

"Does one flake rotate three frames?"

"I don't know."

Even better.

He used wooden tongs, lifted flake into black case.

Frames slowed over maybe thirty breaths.

Not proof.

Maro said exactly that.

"Do not write removal caused stop. Current changed too."

Dema wrote.

I wrote mentally.

Professionals existed everywhere.

Comforting.

Annoying for protagonist ego.

I did not have much protagonist ego.

Probably.

Maro noticed crutches.

Then transit paper.

"Carrow?"

"Yes."

"You with Civic?"

"Temporary walkthrough."

Dema said, "He is being stupid honestly."

Maro nodded like job title normal.

"Useful."

I hated phrase now.

"Is this Glasswake starting early?" I asked.

Maro considered.

"Maybe early pressure. Not wake. River-light crews have seen pre-pull three to eight days before strong years. Also after upper rain. We had upper rain. Don't make prediction from one line."

"Mirror beds?"

"Yes, but don't picture bed like sheet. Pockets, seams, old deposits, worked channels. Some natural, some cut, some seeded."

"Seeded?"

"Old river works. Ask survey office if you want history."

Survey office.

Door touched door.

"Field crew notice? Alder Basin to North Glass Cut?"

Maro looked at me properly now.

"You considering?"

"Considering considering."

Dema said, "That's not sentence."

"It is economically."

Maro closed black case.

"Crew is Vessa Harn's. River Survey. They're short record hand because Len broke wrist unloading gauge box. General support means exactly that until it doesn't."

"Terrain?"

"Cart road to Basin. River skiff two days if current permits. Glass Cut has stone path, uneven, some steps. West Locks mostly built surfaces. One short bank section can be mud."

I looked at crutches.

Maro did too.

Not pity.

Assessment.

"Could I do it?"

"I don't know you."

Correct.

"Would crutches automatically disqualify?"

"No. Vessa will make you walk yard route with loaded record case before she hires. She cares whether you can keep crew pace over required sections and whether you know when to say no."

That was answer.

"What does record hand actually do?"

Maro started counting fingers.

"Copy gauge marks. Hold board. Read labels back. Keep sample tags matched. Time returns. Carry small case. Watch line when told. Cook badly if it's your night. Don't touch river glass unless assigned. Don't improvise around locks. Don't wander because something shiny exists."

Dema looked at me.

"Last one problem?"

"Unknown."

She had known me two hours.

Unfair.

"Why ten to fourteen days?"

"Route depends Glasswake current and lock inspections. Could be ten. Could be sixteen if west return stalls, though notice says fourteen because procurement hates truth."

There.

Important.

"Pay notice says return pass. Return where?"

"Merecross."

Not Carrow.

Good distinction.

"Departure?"

"Morning after Glasswake first night, unless closure changes survey priorities."

Seven days from now roughly.

Time.

Enough to learn city.

Enough to decide.

Not forced tomorrow.

"Application deadline?"

"Vessa starts trials in three days. Fills when she finds someone."

Pressure but not immediate.

Dema said, "You going?"

"I don't know."

"Good."

I glared.

She smiled.

Maro carried flake away.

Lantern frames remained lowered pending review.

Walkthrough resumed.

Now terrace felt different.

Not dangerous.

Alive.

Festival infrastructure responding before festival.

Market adapting.

Workers arguing.

Permits changing.

Visitors arriving despite bridge closure.

At noon Dema signed both sheets.

"Completed."

"Did I pass?"

"No."

"Excellent."

"There is no pass."

"Even better."

Civic runner paid us separately.

Two copper each.

Fourteen to sixteen.

Dema counted hers, put away.

"Lunch?"

Not work invitation maybe.

"Yes."

She took me three lanes away to stall with no sign.

"How do you know food safe?"

"I license them."

Strong.

We ate noodles with fried river greens and something pickled enough to erase childhood.

I paid my own food from bits?

No.

Continuity trap.

Dema said, "My treat. You found twelve-B."

"I didn't."

"You stood there while I found twelve-B."

"Important service."

"Exactly."

She paid.

Bits remained mysterious.

Excellent.

Dema talked more over food.

Not exposition because she mostly complained.

Glasswake brought money and idiots.

Market loved both.

Her licensing office hired seasonal runners every year, half disappeared after two days, one became excellent and then married baker in south district and quit.

She had used cane since fever at eleven left right hip weak under fatigue. Not tragic voice. Fact in story about running after goats during childhood and discovering goats respected no medical limitation.

She hated people touching elbow without asking.

Liked ramps when they went somewhere sensible.

Had never been to Carrow.

"What's there?"

I opened mouth.

Too much.

House.

Theatre.

Cala.

Tavi.

Lyssa.

Hessa.

Vale.

South Quay.

Mara.

Bad lantern.

Good coat.

A city that had become mine by inches.

"People," I said.

Dema waited.

"That's terrible tourism answer."

"I know."

"Anything else?"

"Bridge less important than yours."

She laughed.

"Good."

Damn it.

After lunch she had work.

I had afternoon.

I went River Survey Office.

Not to apply.

Information.

Building sat near north water stairs, low stone with blue-black roof and yard full of poles, cases, folded gauges, skiff parts, ropes, boots drying under awning.

Field work smelled like wet leather everywhere.

First life memory hit hard.

Not exact tools.

Same smell.

Mud, oil, rope, people who expected weather to be rude.

I stood gate longer than necessary.

Clerk looked up.

"Need something?"

"Field crew notice."

"Vessa's?"

"Yes."

"Application?"

"Questions."

"Then wait."

I waited.

Ten minutes.

Then woman came out carrying two record cases at once.

Tall, gray hair braided down back, narrow face, left sleeve rolled above elbow, no patience visible.

"Greg?"

I had not given name.

Clerk had probably asked while I stared at mud.

Less magical again.

"Yes."

"Vessa Harn. Maro sent runner saying a Carrow man on crutches might ask."

Network faster than me.

"I'm not applying yet."

"Then don't waste application paper. Ask."

I liked her too.

Dangerous city.

"Record hand requirements."

She set cases down.

"Read clean. Write clean. Numbers accurately. Repeat instructions. Carry eight-pound case for short sections. Sit cart without needing special suspension. Transfer into river skiff with assistance if requested. Walk uneven ground up to quarter mile at time. Short stone steps. No climbing ladders required for this route. No swimming requirement, but if you cannot tolerate boat, don't come."

Specific.

"Pace?"

"Crew pace isn't march. We stop to work. Problem is getting between points without turning one-hour job into three."

"Crutches?"

"Can you do requirements?"

"Most. Quarter mile uneven depends surface."

"Then yard trial tells some. Basin tells rest."

"What if I can't do a section after hired?"

"If route has cart alternative, use cart. If not and you can't safely do it, we reassign if possible. If job becomes fundamentally different from notice, that's our problem. If you lied about ability, that's yours."

Fair.

"Danger?"

"Road. River. Weather. Locks. Glass cuts. Animals. People."

"Specific."

She stared.

Then gave actual answer.

"No active monster warning on planned route. No bandit notice. River current elevated from upper rain. Glasswake can change navigation windows. North Glass Cut has sharp waste flakes outside maintained path if idiots leave path. West Locks are machinery. We do not enter closed chambers."

Good.

"Artifacts?"

"Survey tools. Some. You don't use anything you're not assigned."

"Personal?"

"Declare active goods. Retired/passive still declare if field-relevant."

Oval.

Interesting.

"I carry one uncertain threshold-related oval. Unrated. No intended field use."

Vessa's expression changed slightly.

"Bring it if you trial. I want to see paperwork or whatever provenance you have. Might tell you leave it here."

Good.

No convenient approval.

"Pay. Six start when?"

"On departure after crew roll. Two per completed field day starting day four. Travel-delay days count if you're still assigned and available. Personal detour doesn't. Return pass to Merecross."

"Food?"

"Crew meals. Bedroll provided if you don't have. Inns where contracted, camps where not."

"How many crew?"

"Five plus record hand."

"Who?"

"Me. Maro Pell river-light. Dask Renn gauges, not your Rell Dask if you're about to ask. Sori Vale, no relation to whatever face you just made. And Kel Toren, driver/boat hand."

Vale.

Universe cruel.

"Sori Vale?"

"Family name. Don't know your people."

"Good."

"Why?"

"Complicated."

"Don't bring complicated unless it carries case."

Excellent.

"Trial in three days?"

"First bell. Yard route. Read-copy test. Case carry. Cart transfer. If you want slot, put name today or tomorrow. No promise I wait."

"Can I watch crew prep without applying?"

"From public line."

"Can I talk to crew?"

"If they're willing and not working."

"Can I see route sheet?"

"Public summary, yes. Detailed work orders after hire."

She handed one page.

Alder Basin.

North Glass Cut.

West Locks.

Then return by west river to Merecross if current and lock windows permit.

Alternate road return if not.

No Carrow.

I read.

First life wanted yes immediately.

Second life wanted to know when letter home would arrive.

Both were me.

Annoying.

"I'll answer tomorrow."

Vessa picked cases up.

"Then answer tomorrow."

No sales pitch.

I left.

On way back, I passed Tessa Vorn's stall.

Night Rest still there.

Twenty-eight copper.

I had sixteen.

Tessa saw me.

"Still poor?"

"Less."

"Dangerous."

"Field crew use these?"

She glanced disk.

"Night Rests? Old ones, sometimes. Survey office retired most. Ask them, not me."

"Vessa Harn crew?"

"She won't let private tools replace assigned reference."

"I was asking whether her crew uses them at all."

"Then ask her."

"Would owning one be useful outside survey?"

"If you need temporary level line under moving light and accept twenty breaths, yes. If you want object to make you interesting, no."

Cruel.

"Demonstration tomorrow night?"

Tessa's gold tooth showed.

"One copper, three objects."

I had sixteen.

One copper for information now reasonable.

But Glasswake pre-pull had begun.

Seller said Night Rest hates blue river nights.

A demo during pre-pull might be more informative and less representative simultaneously.

"Can you demonstrate ordinary behavior away from river influence?"

"Not in Merecross this week."

There.

Constraint.

"Then not yet."

"Smart. Boring."

"I've been called worse."

"Come back."

I did not promise.

At inn, message waited.

Not Carrow.

Transit.

Pavin note:

RETURN CONVOY HOLD EXTENDED 48 HOURS MINIMUM.
SOUTH SPAN CLOSED.
NORTH FORD VEHICLE CLOSED.
CENTER BRIDGE RESTRICTED.
LODGING COVER CONTINUES.

Forty-eight minimum.

I was supposed to be home tonight.

Instead I had job tomorrow? No. Walkthrough completed today.

Tomorrow currently free.

Field trial answer due.

Glasswake in five days after today moved one day closer.

I wrote notebook:

CASH: 16 C
VALE: 23 C
RETURN: +48 HOURS MINIMUM HOLD

DEMA ORR
- MARKET LICENSING
- LEFT FOREARM CANE
- KNOWS LOCAL OPERATIONS / NOT ALL ACCESS
- HATES STUPID RAMPS

FIELD CREW
- VESSA HARN
- 5 + RECORD HAND
- ALDER BASIN / NORTH GLASS CUT / WEST LOCKS
- 10-14 NOTICE, MARO SAYS COULD RUN LONGER
- 6 C START ON DEPARTURE
- +2 C / COMPLETED FIELD DAY STARTING DAY 4
- RETURN PASS = MERECROSS, NOT CARROW
- TRIAL FIRST BELL IN 3 DAYS
- ANSWER TOMORROW IF I WANT SLOT
- 8 LB CASE SHORT SECTIONS
- UNEVEN GROUND UP TO 1/4 MILE AT TIME
- CART / SKIFF TRANSFERS
- DECLARE OVAL IF TRIAL

Then:

QUESTION:
AM I WAITING TO GO HOME OR DECIDING WHERE TO GO NEXT?

I did not answer.

Outside, one lowered festival lantern frame turned slowly on ground.

Maybe wind.

Maybe river.

Maybe badly balanced hoop.

Merecross had enough people qualified to find out.

I had a different problem.

For the first time since leaving Carrow, road north was not theoretical.

It had names.

Outsider.

For one more day, at least.

# CHAPTER 383

## THE APPLICANT

I woke up knowing.

Annoying.

Would have preferred another six hours of complicated self-deception.

Instead I opened eyes in Merecross, heard carts grinding somewhere below window, remembered Alder Basin, and knew I was going to put my name down.

Not take job.

Important.

Trial.

Application.

Possibility.

Several respectable layers between me and stupidity.

I dressed.

Then sat on bed and wrote letter home before I could become brave in wrong order.

CALA / LYSSA

I AM PUTTING MY NAME DOWN FOR A FIELD CREW TRIAL.

Stopped.

Too abrupt.

Accurate though.

I continued.

THIS DOES NOT MEAN I HAVE JOB YET. TRIAL IS IN TWO DAYS. IF HIRED, CREW LEAVES MORNING AFTER FIRST NIGHT OF GLASSWAKE AND ROUTE IS ALDER BASIN / NORTH GLASS CUT / WEST LOCKS, THEN BACK TO MERECROSS. NOTICE SAYS 10-14 DAYS. CREW MEMBER SAYS IT CAN RUN LONGER IF RETURN STALLS.

I stared at longer.

Lyssa would hate longer.

Good reason to include.

PAY IS 6 C AT DEPARTURE + 2 C PER COMPLETED FIELD DAY STARTING DAY 4. MEALS / BEDROLL / RETURN TO MERECROSS COVERED.

I AM ASKING QUESTIONS BEFORE I SAY YES TO ANYTHING.

That sounded defensive.

Was defensive.

I left it.

CALA: MOSTLY CART / SOME SKIFF / SHORT UNEVEN FOOT SECTIONS. THEY HAVE ACTUAL REQUIREMENTS. NO LADDERS ON THIS ROUTE. I WILL NOT LIE ABOUT WHAT I CAN DO.

LYSSA: I AM NOT DISAPPEARING. THIS IS ME TELLING YOU BEFORE I DO IT.

Then Tavi.

TAVI: ROCK IS FINE. IT HAS NOT CONTRIBUTED TO RENT.

Better.

Folded.

At transit office Pavin looked worse.

Not injured.

Clerk worse.

Hair wrong. Ink on cheek. Two cups on desk, both empty.

"Morning."

"Is it?"

"Technically."

"Then yes."

I handed letter.

"East?"

"If east exists."

He checked address names.

"Bell Ford foot runner made it overnight. East cart beyond Ford still moving. This should go noon."

"Reply?"

He looked under stack.

Then stopped.

"Actually."

My stomach did small stupid thing.

Pavin pulled folded packet tied blue thread.

CARROW / GREG
MERECROSS TRANSIT HOLD

Three hands on outside.

Cala's block letters.

Lyssa's narrower slant.

Tavi's crime.

I took it.

Did not open immediately because apparently I enjoyed suffering.

"Anything route?"

Pavin said, "South span still closed. Engineers are doing unloaded return mapping today. North ford foot remains open. Vehicle closed. Center restrictions unchanged. No contracted east convoy for at least tomorrow."

"So still here."

"Unless you leave another way."

There.

Different sentence now.

"I'm applying for River Survey trial."

Pavin nodded once.

"Then tell transit if hired. Your original return coverage doesn't become cash credit."

"Wasn't going to ask."

"People do."

"Can I keep room until departure if hired?"

"Not automatically. Transit covers until contracted return becomes available or alternate authorized. If you voluntarily remain after return available, your lodging becomes yours."

Fair.

"And if route remains closed through field departure?"

"Then coverage likely continues until you change status, but get written determination before crew roll."

Vale everywhere.

I opened packet outside.

Cala first.

GREG,

WE GOT FIRST NOTE YESTERDAY LATE AND SECOND THIS MORNING. HOUSE IS FINE. FLOOR FINISHED. WARDROBE BACK. TAVI TRIED TO ASK IF HE COULD COME BEFORE YOUR SECOND NOTE ARRIVED, SO YOUR WARNING WAS LATE.

I laughed alone on street.

CALA continued.

LYSSA IS HERE AND WRITING HER OWN. DO NOT WORRY ABOUT US BECAUSE YOU ARE AWAY. DO NOT BE STUPID BECAUSE YOU THINK THAT MEANS WE DO NOT WORRY ABOUT YOU.

IF YOU GO FARTHER, SEND ROUTE AND WHO YOU ARE WITH. IF YOU CAN. EAT.

CALA

Reasonable.

Then Tavi.

GREG

ROCK IS YOUR RESPONSIBILITY NOW BECAUSE IT IS TRAVELING.

BRING IT BACK.

ALSO YOU.

TAVI

That one hurt more.

Tiny bastard.

Lyssa last.

GREG,

I was going to write a normal letter and Cala told me not to lie in writing.

So.

I hate that you are farther away than I expected.

I also know you.

If you come home only because you think I need you to prove you would, I will be angry.

If you go farther and don't tell me, I will be angrier.

If something changes, tell me. If you choose something, tell me that too. There is a difference.

My heat-cloth sample passed. Marra accepted the lot. I took the third lot. You are missing me complain about treated weave. This is your punishment.

I miss you.

Go see the stupid river light if you are there for it.

Come back when you come back.

LYSSA

Then squeezed beneath margin:

DO NOT BUY A 28 COPPER THING JUST BECAUSE YOU ARE SAD.

I stared.

How the fuck.

Second note did not mention Night Rest.

First didn't.

I had not told them.

I checked packet backside.

Cala added tiny line:

P.S. Vale sent separate note. He says you copied a disposal board and apparently this means he knows what kind of market you are near. He told Lyssa not to ask price. She asked price.

Traitor.

I laughed again.

People passed.

Merecross did not care.

Good.

I read Lyssa twice.

Come back when you come back.

Not permission.

Did not need permission.

Still felt like hand opening instead of holding.

I folded packet carefully and put inside shoulder bag away from oval.

Then River Survey.

Vessa was in yard arguing with a wheel.

Not person named Wheel.

Actual wheel.

"It isn't round enough," she said.

Kel Toren, presumably, crouched by cart axle.

"It's round."

"Not under load."

"Everything stops being round under enough load."

"Then choose amount where ours remains wheel."

Kel looked up at me.

Mid-forties maybe. Wide face. Hair tied in three short knots for reasons unknown.

"Applicant?"

Vessa turned.

"Are you?"

"Yes. Trial."

There.

Said.

No thunder.

No destiny.

Vessa pointed clerk table.

"Name. Carrow residence. Emergency contact if reachable. Relevant injuries or mobility constraints. Reading/writing declaration. Artifact declaration."

"Emergency contact?"

"Someone who should get bad news eventually."

Horrible phrasing.

Useful.

I wrote Cala household first.

Then Lyssa.

Paused.

Relationship category line.

No good word.

I wrote:

PERSON TO NOTIFY: LYSSA

Enough.

Mobility:

LEFT BELOW-KNEE AMPUTATION. TWO CRUTCHES. RIGHT LEG INTACT. NO CURRENT WOUND. UNEVEN GROUND SPEED DEPENDS SURFACE. CAN STATE LIMITS.

Artifact:

ONE SMALL OVAL, UNCERTAIN THRESHOLD / RETREAT-RELATED BEHAVIOR. UNRATED. NO INTENDED FIELD USE. PAPER NOTES PARTIAL. WILL PRESENT AT TRIAL.

Vessa read.

"Why bring it at all?"

"Regular bag equipment."

"Why?"

Fair.

"Because it has shown enough threshold-related behavior that I prefer having it to not having it, but not enough that I obey it blindly."

"That's bad answer and maybe honest one."

"Yes."

"Bring it wrapped. Do not activate."

"I don't know how to activate it deliberately."

"Even better reason not to try."

She initialed.

Application done.

"Trial first bell day after tomorrow. Eat first. Bring normal travel carry, not empty bag designed to impress me."

"How much?"

"What you'd actually carry on route personal, minus crew-issued bedroll and food."

"I don't know yet."

"Then figure out tomorrow."

"Eight-pound case separate?"

"Yes."

"Can I use porter harness?"

"If it's your normal equipment."

"Replacement buckle not trusted above roughly ten to twelve pounds."

"Then don't make it carry twelve."

Simple.

Kel snorted.

Vessa looked at him.

"Wheel."

He returned to wheel.

Application apparently concluded.

I should leave.

Instead I asked, "Can I watch prep from public line?"

"You asked yesterday. Answer remains yes."

Right.

Memory existed.

I went public line.

Crew yard activity had shape now.

Cases sorted by painted marks.

Blue lids gauges.

Black river-light.

Red repair.

Plain wood food and camp.

Not universal system. This crew.

Maro sat at bench cleaning black tongs from yesterday.

He saw me.

"Applied?"

"Trial."

"Good."

"Why good?"

"Because now Vessa can reject you based on evidence."

Soft-spoken bastard.

"Comforting."

"Should be."

He held up tongs.

"Want to know what yesterday's flake was?"

"Yes."

"Still old mirror flake."

"Excellent update."

"We found storage bin missing corner seal. Same flake type inside. Likely storage contamination. Likely is not certain."

"Frames?"

"Rotated again this morning with no flake found."

There.

Good.

Not magic shard culprit.

"So pre-pull."

"Probably."

"Strong year?"

"Stop."

I stopped.

Maro smiled faintly.

Learning relationship.

Another crew member came carrying brass rod bundle.

Young woman, maybe twenty-five, hair shaved at temples, rest tied high. She saw me, saw application table, saw crutches.

"You're trying Len's slot?"

"Yes."

"Can you write numbers that look like numbers?"

"Usually."

"Better than Len."

Maro said, "Len's not here to defend handwriting."

"That's why I chose now."

She set rods down.

"Sori Vale. No relation to your face."

Second person.

"Greg."

"I know. Vessa said Carrow Greg."

"How many Carrow Gregs?"

"Today? One."

Not dry exactly.

Literal enough.

"What do you do?"

"Lock records, permits, route copies, sometimes sample custody, sometimes tell Dask Renn he cannot invent abbreviations because he gets bored."

"So records too."

"Different records. You would write field sheet. I make sure field sheet remains legally attached to reality afterward."

Vale energy.

Unrelated supposedly.

"Do you like job?"

Sori looked offended by broad question.

"Parts."

Then she explained for three minutes.

Liked travel mornings, hated wet paper, liked locks when functioning, hated lock supervisors who thought permit meant suggestion, loved North Glass Cut except shoes, disliked Alder Basin mosquitoes, had no opinion about West Locks food because "food there has no opinion about itself."

Messier than first impression.

Good.

"What makes record hand fail?"

She answered immediately.

"Trying to look useful instead of being useful."

That landed.

"Meaning?"

"They carry things nobody asked for. Touch tags. Guess spellings instead of asking. Write from memory later because embarrassed they missed line. Say yes when they didn't hear. Wander ahead. Or think general support means junior surveyor."

"And physical pace?"

"Vessa decides. Kel complains about everyone equally."

From wheel: "True."

Sori continued.

"If you need help transferring skiff, say before we're holding skiff in current. If you need cart stop, say before you vomit. If you need ten breaths after slope, say ten breaths, not 'I'm fine' until you're useless for twenty minutes."

Specific social contract.

I knew that one.

First life had punished pretending fine.

Second body more honest.

Usually.

"Anything people don't ask?"

Sori thought.

"Being away is boring more than dangerous. Then suddenly not. Bring one thing to read. Don't bring six."

"Good."

"Also Vessa snores."

"Sori."

Vessa across yard.

"What? It's field-relevant."

I left before becoming employed socially.

Merecross afternoon had brightened.

Not sun.

River.

Subtle.

At first I thought cloud gap.

Then noticed undersides of awnings carried pale silver-blue bands.

Not reflected sky.

Moving wrong direction.

People noticed but did not stop work.

Children did.

Of course.

They chased light along walls.

Shopkeepers began hanging darker cloth behind mirror displays.

Glasswake pre-pull stronger.

Five days? Four now maybe.

Festival countdown slippery because first cold current did not care schedule.

I went North Market without deciding to.

Tessa was covering half her yellow-lane shelf with gray cloth.

"You're back."

"River brighter."

"Excellent eyes."

"Night Rest?"

Still uncovered.

Cloudy glass center now showed thin pale line.

Not horizontal.

Tilted maybe ten degrees.

Object sat flat-looking table.

I stopped.

"Is that set?"

Tessa did not touch.

"No."

"Card says after set."

"Yes."

"Then why line?"

"Glasswake influence. Told you it wanders."

"Does it normally show anything unset?"

"Not ordinary week."

Seller claim.

But I was witnessing current behavior now.

Line shifted while I watched.

Slow.

Maybe one degree over ten breaths.

No touching.

No demo fee because public shelf doing weird thing.

Tessa said, "This is why locals don't want old North Channel aids during wake. They start answering river instead of user."

"Answering how?"

"Poetic word. Don't quote me technically."

Good.

"Price still twenty-eight?"

"Yes."

"Even while malfunctioning publicly?"

"Especially while visitors think malfunction is interesting."

Rational seller.

"You said twenty-two after festival if unsold."

"If unsold."

"Could sell because this."

"Yes."

"I dislike you."

"No you don't."

Correct.

A man beside me asked price.

Tessa said twenty-eight.

He asked twenty-four.

She said twenty-eight.

He left.

Market awake.

I did not bid.

Sixteen copper remained sixteen.

I wanted object more now.

Bad.

But field crew pay suddenly connected.

If hired:

6c start = 22.

Then field days.

Potentially enough.

Except object might sell.

And earning money to buy object was not same as object waiting for me.

Good economy.

World rude.

I wrote no note because already had card details.

Then I went river rail.

Not terrace work.

Just looked.

Merecross river had begun doing something I had never seen.

Not Glasswake full.

Pre-pull.

Light moved under surface like long pale threads dragged upstream while water flowed down.

Could be refraction.

Could be magical mineral sheets.

Could be both because distinction stupid here.

A barge sat tied under center bridge waiting permit.

Its shadow had a bright edge underneath.

People around me treated it like first cold smell before snow.

Not extraordinary exactly.

Season arriving.

I thought of Carrow sky ribbon.

Different.

Do not connect because pretty.

I thought of Lyssa saying go see stupid river light.

So I did.

No one beside me knew her.

No one knew Cala.

No one knew Tavi.

No one knew I had five artifacts, four stupidly left at home, which was correct.

No one knew Vale debt.

No one knew Hessa would kill me verbally if I called moving light mana without evidence.

I was nobody here except what I did next.

That felt familiar from first life.

But first life I had liked it because nothing followed me.

Now things followed.

Letters.

People.

A rock in my bag.

I took rock out.

White stripe caught river light.

"You have visited," I told it.

Woman nearby moved away slightly.

Fair.

I put rock back.

At inn, another notice from Civic waited.

Iven offered second walkthrough, West Steps, tomorrow afternoon if Greg wanted, same 2c, local partner different.

I considered.

Trial day after tomorrow.

Need pack honestly.

Need rest leg.

Need maybe not monetize every hour.

I wrote decline for now.

Not because money irrelevant.

Because sixteen copper plus possible field work meant one extra 2c did not own tomorrow automatically.

Economic progression again.

I ate included stew.

Pepper remained criminal.

Then notebook.

APPLICATION: FILED
TRIAL: DAY AFTER TOMORROW, FIRST BELL
FIELD JOB: NOT YET MINE

CASH: 16 C
VALE: 23 C

HOME LETTER RECEIVED
- HOUSE FINE
- TAVI DEMANDS ROCK + ME RETURN
- LYSSA THIRD LOT ACCEPTED / HEAT SAMPLE PASSED
- LYSSA: IF CHOOSE SOMETHING, TELL HER

I paused.

Then copied one line exactly because needed.

COME BACK WHEN YOU COME BACK.

Under that:

CHOICE MADE TODAY:
TRY.

Not go.

Not leave.

Try.

Small word.

Huge door.

Applicant.

For once, I had applied to somewhere farther away.

# CHAPTER 384

## THE VOLUNTEER

I packed badly on purpose.

First attempt.

Everything I thought I might want went on bed.

Shoulder bag.

Porter harness.

Spare shirt.

Second spare shirt because apparently wilderness consumed shirts.

Notebook.

Two pencils.

Knife.

Small repair wrap.

Extra sock.

Other extra sock.

One was enough because anatomy had become economical in specific categories.

Soap.

Comb.

Folded Carrow letters.

Tavi's rock.

Oval wrapped separately.

Transit papers.

Field application copy.

Coin purse.

A spoon I had somehow acquired from inn breakfast.

I stared at spoon.

"No."

Put it back downstairs.

Innkeeper looked at me like theft confession disappointing.

Second attempt.

One spare shirt.

One spare sock.

Notebook.

Pencils.

Knife.

Repair wrap.

Soap sliver.

Comb.

Letters.

Rock.

Oval.

Papers.

Money.

Harness.

Water flask empty for weighing logic, would be filled travel day.

Still too much in shoulder bag because shoulder bag had become place where objects went to avoid decisions.

I moved heavier mundane pieces to right-side harness.

Not oval.

Not letters.

Not rock.

Rock weighed almost nothing.

Emotionally six pounds.

I walked inn corridor.

Down stairs.

Across common room.

Outside.

One block.

Back.

Right harness sat fine.

Shoulder bag strap pulled left enough to annoy crutch rhythm.

Problem.

I removed comb.

Walked again.

Problem remained because comb was not secretly iron bar.

Good diagnosis.

Moved notebook to harness.

Better.

Letters stayed shoulder bag.

Oval stayed shoulder bag.

Money stayed on body.

Papers stayed inner pocket.

I could have optimized another hour.

Instead I stopped.

Trial tomorrow.

Today was not trial.

Outside, Merecross had become brighter under clouds.

Not daylight brighter.

Wrong brighter.

Pale bands moved across second-story walls as river threw light upward between buildings.

People had started hanging black and rust-red cloth over windows facing water.

Festival crews loved it.

Regular residents looked tired.

I had no work.

This was intentional.

Disturbing.

I went south.

Not market.

Not Survey Office.

Not Civic Hall.

Just south.

Merecross narrowed away from river into older lanes where houses leaned toward each other and drains ran open beside curb. Laundry bridges crossed alleys overhead. Someone had painted fish on every third door.

No explanation.

Good.

I bought nothing.

Also disturbing.

At a small square, six children were throwing flat wooden rings at upright pegs.

One ring missed and rolled toward me.

I stopped with crutch.

Child shouted, "Don't step on it!"

"Excellent advice generally."

He retrieved ring.

"You can play."

"I am extremely busy."

I looked around empty square.

He looked too.

"Doing what?"

"Maintaining mystery."

"You're bad at it."

Fair.

I played.

Rules were not what I thought.

Of course.

Three pegs.

Near peg worth one.

Far peg worth two.

Middle peg worth nothing but let you throw again.

Why middle existed?

Because founder was drunk, according to oldest girl.

Probably false.

I lost to seven-year-old named Rima who threw sideways and screamed before ring landed.

Then square changed.

Every metal drain grate began humming.

Not loud.

Low enough first felt in crutch tips.

Children stopped.

Adults did not panic.

They did move.

Shop shutters opened.

One woman yelled, "Glass run! Get chalk!"

Children scattered with terrifying competence.

I stood.

Rima grabbed my sleeve.

"Not middle."

"What?"

"Street middle."

She pointed.

Pale line appeared along gutter water.

Then another.

Light, not liquid.

It ran uphill.

Fast.

I moved to wall with everyone else.

No one needed me.

Good.

A man came out carrying bucket of white chalk chunks.

People marked door thresholds with short horizontal lines.

Not magic ward maybe.

I did not assume.

Woman beside me noticed face.

"Shows where shine crossed after."

Record.

Not protection.

"Why?"

"Drain office asks."

"Every time?"

"First runs."

Pale gutter line reached square.

Metal grates hummed harder.

Then light split around central fountain base.

One branch went east drain.

One climbed wet seam between paving stones toward bakery.

That got attention.

"That's new," someone said.

Bad phrase.

Bakery owner swore and pulled flour sacks off floor.

Light reached wall.

Stopped.

Then rose vertically maybe two feet through damp stone seam.

Beautiful.

Wrong.

A child moved closer.

Three adults said no at once.

Good city.

I stayed wall.

Could have gone closer.

Did not.

First-life field instincts said observe path, material, timing, change.

Second-life curiosity said shiny.

Both stayed put.

The line faded after maybe a minute.

Grates stopped humming.

Everyone exhaled without admitting.

Then work began.

Chalk marks.

Questions.

Someone sent runner.

Bakery owner checked wall dry cloth.

Rima resumed game.

"That's it?" I asked.

"Sometimes it comes back."

"Comforting."

"You throw now."

Children.

I threw.

Missed everything.

Drain office arrived ten minutes later.

Not Maro.

Not Dema.

Two workers in green aprons, one carrying measuring cord, other shallow black tray.

They followed chalk marks, asked witnesses where first light appeared, did not touch wall until bakery owner confirmed no heat.

I was witness maybe.

Then one worker frowned at paving seam.

"Who saw branch start?"

Several people pointed vaguely.

I had watched from angle.

"At fountain base. It split around north edge, east branch stayed gutter, west branch took wet paving seam."

Worker looked.

"You sure west?"

I oriented.

"Sorry. South-west from fountain. Toward bakery."

"How fast?"

"Faster than walking, slower than running?"

Terrible measurement.

"Useful enough."

He marked.

Other worker asked, "Did it cross dry stone?"

"I couldn't tell. Seam looked wet from where I stood."

"Good."

No embellishment.

Sori would approve.

Maybe.

Worker placed black tray over seam after event, not during. Tray had dull sand inside.

"What's that?"

"Moisture comparison."

Mundane.

"Oh."

He smiled.

"Sorry."

"No, that's healthier."

Then somebody screamed from next lane.

Actual scream.

We moved.

Not sprinting heroically.

I had crutches.

Reality remained.

Around corner, festival delivery cart had lost rear wheel.

Not Glasswake monster.

Wheel axle pin sheared while cart crossed lane hump. Cart tilted hard right. Three stacked crates of mirror lantern panes leaned against rope side.

One rope had slipped.

Driver underneath? No.

Horse forward, frightened but held by two people.

A porter had foot trapped between fallen crate and curb.

There.

Physical.

People already helping.

I stopped outside immediate cluster.

"Need hands?"

Man at crate shouted, "Need lift!"

I could not safely two-hand lift while standing on crutches.

Different.

"I can brace rope."

"Then brace!"

Rope ran from upper crate stack to left cart rail.

I moved right side where wall ring anchored awning line.

Not rated for cart.

No.

Do not invent anchor.

Instead wrapped rope around intact stone bollard at lane edge.

Bollard looked structural but appearance not enough.

"This bollard for carts?"

Driver yelled, "Tie post! Yes!"

Good.

I took slack by leaning body/harness, not crutch shaft.

Porter harness suddenly useful.

Rope pressure came across right hip/side.

I did not haul crate up.

I kept stack from shifting farther while four people levered lower crate with wooden bar.

Trapped porter shouted instructions nobody needed.

"Not my toes! Not my fucking toes!"

"We know," woman said.

"I need them!"

"We know!"

"All of them!"

Messy speech under fear.

Good.

Crate lifted inch.

Porter pulled foot.

Then crate slipped.

Rope snapped tight.

Harness bit.

Right leg held.

Crutch tips skidded half inch.

I said, "Stop."

People stopped.

Important.

"Reset me."

No shame.

Woman nearest took rope load with both hands while I reset right crutch farther back.

"Ready."

Again.

This time lever block under crate before lift.

Porter free.

Foot bloody at side but not flattened. He could move toes and immediately tried stand.

Everyone told him not to.

He argued.

Naturally.

Green-apron drain worker became medical-adjacent only enough to say sit, pressure, runner for healer.

No fake diagnosis.

Mirror-pane crates stayed intact.

Cart did not.

Driver looked at broken wheel.

"That was new pin."

Kel would have opinions.

I did not.

Rope had left red pressure stripe across harness padding.

No tear.

Buckle held.

Load had been dynamic briefly.

Unknown exact force.

Do not convert into rating.

I loosened harness and checked stitch line.

Fine visually.

Still would inspect before trial.

Driver noticed.

"You hurt?"

"No. Harness took pull."

"Good brace."

"Good bollard."

He laughed once, shaky.

Then looked porter and stopped laughing.

Healer arrived.

I became unnecessary.

Excellent.

I left before someone invented job.

At next corner Dema found me.

Of course.

"Why are you holding your side?"

"Cart."

"That's not answer."

"Cart tried to become floor."

"Better."

I explained.

She looked harness.

"Trial tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"You injured?"

"No."

"Actually?"

I moved right shoulder, hip, planted crutches.

"Actually no. Pressure stripe. Harness took load."

"Then why face?"

"Because I have to decide whether harness is still normal equipment after it caught dynamic load."

Dema nodded.

"Ask someone who knows harness."

"Yes."

"Then don't stop at your own inspection."

"I was considering it."

"Don't."

Annoying city.

"Know harness person?"

"Three. One owes me because his permit board was wrong for two months and I didn't fine him because handwriting looked like dying spider."

"That sounds like reason to fine more."

"Come on."

We went two lanes east.

Harness shop was narrow and smelled of oil leather.

Owner **Baret Oss** had beard split into two braids and spoke to harness before people.

"Used?"

"Today. Brief brace on cart crate rope. Dynamic snap once."

"How much?"

"Unknown. Enough to shift my crutch tips slightly."

"Bad unit."

"Yes."

He took harness.

Checked buckle first.

Replacement buckle.

Then stitching.

Then strap where rope pressure had loaded body through harness.

"What is normal rating?"

"Carry. Replacement buckle I was told caution above roughly ten to twelve pounds."

Baret grunted.

"Carry load and shock load are cousins who hate each other."

Bad metaphor.

Good.

He bent buckle tongue under thumb.

"No visible set. Stitch hasn't walked. Leather stretched here maybe hair. Could be old. I cannot certify because I didn't make repair and don't know original leather."

"Would you carry six pounds tomorrow?"

"Me? Yes. You? I don't know how you fall."

Excellent.

"Can you reinforce?"

"Can replace buckle and sister strap. Two copper, done evening. But that solves known weak point, not turns whole thing into rescue harness."

Two copper.

I had sixteen.

Trial mattered.

Harness mattered.

This was exactly what money was for.

"Do it."

Sixteen to fourteen.

No agony.

Progress.

Baret wrote receipt.

"Pickup last bell."

Dema looked pleased.

"Don't."

"I'm not."

"Your face is."

"My face thinks you're trainable."

"I regret lunch."

"No refunds."

We walked out.

I asked about drain light.

Dema said first Glass runs often found wet seams and old reflective residue in drains, but uphill branch into bakery wall was unusual enough Drain Office would log. She did not know mechanism.

"Dangerous?"

"Usually no. Sometimes heat if trapped near metal grate. Sometimes people stare into bright reflection until they fall over something ordinary."

"Strong city tradition."

"Falling over ordinary things? Ancient."

We reached river lane.

Dema had work.

I had nothing again.

"You volunteering for carts now?"

"No."

"Good."

"I helped."

"Different."

There.

She left.

I spent afternoon doing almost nothing productive.

Sat under awning.

Watched river light strengthen.

Read one of inn's terrible serialized pamphlets about noblewoman secretly becoming swordmaster despite cover illustration clearly showing her holding sword on page one.

Sori said bring one thing to read.

I considered stealing pamphlet.

Then remembered spoon.

Growth.

At last bell I collected harness.

Baret had replaced buckle with heavier ordinary brass and stitched sister strip along stressed section.

"Carry use. Not climbing. Not rescue. Not anchor. Not proof you can catch cart."

"Understood."

"Say it."

"Carry use only."

"Good."

He had me load six-pound shop weight and walk lane.

No shift.

Eight pounds.

Still stable.

He stopped there.

"Could test more."

"Why?"

Good.

I paid 2c.

Fourteen remained.

At inn I repacked.

Harness: notebook, repair wrap, spare shirt, soap, small mundane pieces.

Shoulder bag: letters, oval wrapped, Tavi rock, immediate papers.

Money on body.

Water tomorrow.

No spoon.

I wrote:

HARNESS
- CART INCIDENT / BRIEF DYNAMIC LOAD
- NO INJURY
- BARET OSS REPLACED BUCKLE + SISTER STRAP, 2 C
- WALKED 6 LB + 8 LB AFTER REPAIR
- CARRY USE ONLY
- NOT RESCUE / CLIMB / ANCHOR

CASH: 14 C
VALE: 23 C

Then:

GLASS RUN
- DRAIN LIGHT UPHILL
- WET SEAM? LOGGED BY DRAIN OFFICE
- DO NOT PRETEND I UNDERSTAND

Then trial.

FIRST BELL.
EAT FIRST.
NORMAL LOAD.
OVAL WRAPPED.
SAY WHEN I NEED TEN BREATHS.

I looked at word volunteer.

Today I had helped because I was there.

No pay.

No contract.

No one owed me adventure.

That felt good too.

Volunteer.

Tomorrow somebody got to decide whether I was useful on purpose.

# CHAPTER 385

## THE CANDIDATE

I ate first.

This was apparently character development.

Eggs.

Bread.

Something the inn called river sausage despite no evidence sausage had personally entered river.

Two cups water.

Then normal load.

Repaired porter harness on right.

Shoulder bag light enough not to drag left crutch rhythm.

Oval wrapped.

Letters wrapped separately because priorities.

Tavi's rock in side pocket.

No spoon.

First bell had not rung when I reached River Survey yard.

Vessa was already there.

Of course.

Kel was there too, leaning against cart with expression of man who had lost argument to wheel and intended revenge later.

Maro sat on crate drinking something steaming.

Sori had three boards under one arm and was telling clerk that if form said west lock singular one more time she would personally build second lock just to make paperwork true.

New man stood beside chalk table.

Thin.

Long neck.

Copper spectacles with one lens slightly darker than other.

Hair tied back badly enough that several pieces had escaped and were now independent.

He held three pencils behind left ear.

One in hand.

Another between teeth.

"Greg," Vessa said.

"Vessa."

She looked at bag.

Harness.

Crutches.

"Breakfast?"

"Yes."

"Water?"

"Yes."

"Pain?"

"Normal hands. Right side a little aware of yesterday's cart brace, not painful. No leg issue."

"Harness?"

"Inspected and repaired by Baret Oss. New buckle, sister strap. Two copper. Walked six and eight pounds after repair. Carry use only. Not rescue, climbing, anchor, or dynamic load."

Kel stopped leaning.

"Yesterday's cart?"

"You heard?"

"Wheel failed. Of course I heard."

He looked personally betrayed by distant axle pin.

"Driver says new pin."

"New is age, not quality."

There.

He had been waiting.

Vessa pointed at thin man.

"Dask Renn. Gauges."

Dask removed pencil from mouth.

"Your handwriting?"

"Mine."

"Good or bad?"

"Readable."

"People who say readable mean bad."

"Then excellent."

"People who say excellent mean liar."

"You seem difficult to satisfy."

"That's because numbers have enemies."

Sori walked past.

"He means handwriting."

"I mean approximation."

"You also mean handwriting."

Dask put pencil back between teeth.

Apparently introduction complete.

Vessa held hand out.

"Oval."

I took wrapped bundle from shoulder bag and gave it to her.

Did not unwrap.

She did not either.

"Notes."

I handed copied page.

She read.

Maro came over because artifact-shaped problem had appeared in gravitational range.

Vessa passed notes to him.

He read longer.

"You call it threshold-related," he said.

"Because observed behavior clustered around threshold or retreat contexts. Not because mechanism established."

"Good."

"It has shown no-crossing behavior?"

"Something like refusal or resistance at least once. I don't trust exact category."

"Deliberate activation?"

"No."

"Known river response?"

"No."

"Glass response?"

"No."

Maro handed page back to Vessa.

"I'd leave it."

There.

No adventure convenience.

I felt immediate resistance.

Interesting.

"Why?"

Maro answered, "Because North Glass Cut and West Locks both contain controlled thresholds, flow gates, return markers, and old reflective infrastructure. Your object has uncertain threshold behavior and no river-context history. It may do nothing. That's likely. But you gain little by carrying unknown interaction into a route already full of known systems."

"What if it would be useful?"

"Useful at what?"

I had no answer.

Exactly.

Vessa said, "If hired, leave it in Merecross sealed with transit or licensed storage."

"Can I trial with it in bag?"

"No."

I took bundle back.

Not offended.

Slightly offended.

Different.

"Where store during trial?"

Vessa pointed clerk lockbox.

I signed temporary receipt.

Oval went in.

Door closed.

Adventure inventory reduced by one mysterious object.

Probably healthy.

Vessa looked at yard clock.

"Candidate two is late."

Candidate two.

Good.

I was not alone.

"How many?"

"Two scheduled."

"Do we compete?"

"You both meet job or don't. If both meet, I choose. If neither, I keep looking."

Simple.

Bell rang.

Gate remained empty.

Vessa waited five minutes.

Not one.

Five.

Then marked board.

"Start."

"What about other candidate?"

"Late is information. If they arrive, they can explain."

No dramatic disqualification speech.

I liked that.

First test was writing.

Of course Dask supervised.

He gave me weathered field tag.

Not magical.

Numbers, letters, two scratches, one faded stamp.

"Copy exactly what you can establish. Mark uncertainty. Do not improve ugly source."

I sat.

Tag read:

NC-7 / EAST CUT
GAUGE 4
RETURN 11?

Last digit could be seven.

Could be one with stain.

Stamp corner missing.

I copied.

RETURN 11? [SECOND DIGIT UNCERTAIN: POSS. 7]

Dask looked.

"Why not choose?"

"Because I can't establish."

"Why possible seven?"

"Upper diagonal could be ink stroke. Could be stain edge."

"Good."

He gave second.

Then third.

Second easy.

Third had old abbreviation I did not know.

I copied abbreviation rather than expanding.

Dask asked meaning.

"Don't know."

"Excellent."

First praise.

Apparently ignorance had career path.

Then read-back.

Sori stood ten feet away and called six entries from board.

I repeated.

She intentionally changed one on confirmation.
