"North gate four?"

"You said North gauge four."

She looked at Vessa.

"Good."

Not trick exactly.

Error-pressure.

Then instructions.

Vessa gave seven steps once.

Take blue case.

Carry to west peg.

Set beside peg, not on chalk.

Copy tag from case lid to board.

Return by gravel lane.

At red marker stop and read board back.

Do not open case.

If footing uncertain, stop.

I repeated.

Missed beside peg.

Said set at west peg.

Vessa said, "Again."

She repeated whole instruction.

I repeated correctly.

No shame.

Blue case weighed eight pounds.

Separate from normal personal load.

I lifted with right hand.

Problem immediate.

Hand carrying case meant right crutch unavailable.

Obviously.

I looked at case handle.

Harness.

Vessa waited.

"How is record hand supposed to carry this while walking with two crutches?"

"Candidate decides."

Not helpful.

I could hang case from harness.

But harness already carried personal load.

Eight-pound test weight plus personal load might exceed sensible repaired system context depending distribution.

Could shift personal bag.

Could ask accommodation.

Could carry case short distance by hand while using one crutch and wall?

Bad.

"I need either case attached to approved carry point or someone carries my personal harness load while I carry case on harness. I won't one-crutch gravel with eight pounds in hand."

Vessa nodded.

"Choose."

"Move personal harness load temporarily to cart. Case on right harness if attachment geometry works and total case load is eight."

Kel brought short rated strap.

"Crew case strap."

"Normal equipment?"

"Crew-issued."

Good.

We attached blue case high enough not to strike right crutch.

Eight pounds.

Baret had walked harness at eight after repair.

Same weight, different shape.

Not same proof.

I tested three steps.

Case swung.

"No."

Stopped.

Kel adjusted lower keeper strap to thigh-side loop.

"Again."

Three steps.

Better.

"Acceptable."

Vessa said, "Then route."

West peg.

Packed dirt first.

Then boards.

Then gravel lane.

I planted shorter than usual.

Case changed right-side momentum.

Not huge.

Enough.

West peg reached.

Set beside peg.

Not chalk.

Copied lid tag.

Returned by gravel.

Halfway, loose patch under right crutch shifted.

Not fall.

Tip moved maybe inch.

I stopped.

Reset.

Ten breaths? No.

Two enough.

Continued.

At red marker stopped.

Read board back.

Dask corrected one spacing mark that apparently mattered to him spiritually but not to field result.

Sori said, "It matters because two marks together become another mark."

Dask pointed at her.

"See?"

"I was defending system, not you."

"Same thing."

"Absolutely not."

Crew had history.

Good.

Vessa asked, "Body?"

"Hands good. Right hip notices case swing. No pain."

"Again, different route."

Second route included low timber step, packed slope, narrow turn between stacked empty crates, and rough cobble patch.

Not obstacle course.

Yard.

Real enough.

I did it slower.

Too slow maybe.

Vessa timed but did not announce.

At slope top I stopped.

"Ten breaths."

Kel looked at me.

Not annoyed.

Counting maybe.

I took eight.

"Ready."

Vessa said, "You asked ten."

"I need eight."

"Then say eight."

Right.

"Eight."

We continued.

At cobble, case keeper strap rubbed side.

Not unsafe.

Annoying.

I finished.

Vessa wrote.

No expression.

"Time?"

"Later."

"Good or bad?"

"Later."

Hessa with logistics.

Terrible.

Cart transfer next.

Kel handled.

Survey cart had higher floor than passenger coach and narrow iron step.

"Show how you'd get in. Don't perform. If you need hand, ask."

I set crutches against rail one at time, tested step with right foot, used side grab.

Height awkward.

Could do.

Left residual limb had no useful foot placement obviously.

I pulled with arms, drove right leg, got hip to bench.

Ugly.

Stable.

Kel said, "Again with wet rail assumption."

"Meaning don't trust rail grip?"

"Meaning rail may be wet. Use strap."

He showed fixed boarding strap under seat edge.

I tried.

Better.

"Out."

I got out.

Crutch retrieval was annoying.

Kel showed socket where crew could secure them before transfer.

"Would someone have to place them?"

"Usually whoever boards behind you. Or you can reach socket from ground if cart isn't loaded. Loaded, sacks block."

"Then I ask."

"Yes."

No independence theater.

Dock next.

Not full boat ride.

Vessa had never promised.

Survey skiff tied in protected side cut behind office.

Current beyond wall visibly stronger.

Skiff moved anyway.

Kel stepped in first.

"This is transfer check, not balance contest. Crutches hand to me or Sori. You use dock post and my forearm if needed."

I handed crutches to Sori.

That felt wrong every time.

Not emotionally.

Physically.

Hands empty meant options gone.

I sat dock edge, right foot to skiff floor when Kel held boat close, left knee clear, one hand post, one Kel forearm.

Transferred.

Boat moved three inches away at worst possible time.

Kel pulled it back with line.

I froze instead of chasing.

Good.

Then in.

Bench.

Crutches passed.

"Out."

Reverse harder.

I asked Kel, "Can you hold hull tight until right foot loaded?"

"Yes."

He did.

Out.

No drama.

My heart disagreed.

Vessa wrote.

"Boat tolerance?"

"Fine. Transfer needs assistance."

"Expected."

There.

Not bonus.

Not failure.

Back in yard, second candidate had arrived.

Young man maybe twenty.

Both boots.

Broad shoulders.

Breathing hard from run.

He stood at clerk table talking fast.

"I know, I know, first bell, but north lane got held because a glass line crossed the cart gate and they stopped everybody, then I went east around tannery and tannery lane is shut because somebody dropped lime barrel, and I should've left earlier, that's mine, but I didn't just sleep, I swear."

Vessa listened.

"Name?"

"Orin Pell."

Another Pell.

Merecross had Pells like Carrow had bad stairs.

"Scheduled?"

"Yes."

"You are forty-three minutes late."

"I know."

"Do you still want trial?"

"Yes."

"Then eat."

Orin blinked.

"What?"

"Did you eat?"

"No."

"Kitchen shelf. Bread. Five minutes. Then Dask."

Not disqualified.

Information.

He looked at me.

"You other one?"

"Greg."

"Orin. Sorry."

"For what?"

"Being late."

"Didn't affect me."

"Still."

He disappeared toward bread.

I liked him enough to be inconvenient.

Vessa said, "Break."

I sat crate.

Water.

Hands warm.

Shoulders worked.

Right hip okay.

No magic used.

Good.

Dask set a small brass gauge marker on crate beside me while rearranging boards.

Round disk, maybe two fingers wide, little raised stem.

He turned away.

Crate wasn't level.

Disk began rolling.

Slow.

Toward edge.

I saw it.

Dask didn't.

Could have said something.

Could have reached.

Too far seated.

Could let it fall.

Probably fine.

Instead old reflex moved before committee formed.

External.

Tiny.

No setup.

No bead.

No broad return side.

No Hessa telling me where to stand.

I was seated, torso twisted, target moving, maybe a pace and a half away.

Push.

Disk changed direction.

Not much.

Enough.

It curved away from crate edge and rattled flat against Dask's board.

I froze.

Dask turned.

Looked at disk.

Looked at me.

Maro looked too.

Of course he had seen.

"You cast?" Dask asked.

"Yes."

"At my gauge marker?"

"It was falling."

"It is brass."

"Congratulations."

"No, I mean it would've survived."

"Then I saved it from emotional damage."

Dask picked disk up.

"Do that again."

Hessa appeared in my skull.

Do not reproduce it.

"No."

Dask stared.

"Why?"

"Because I don't know if I can, and chasing one clean result immediately is how I turn useful evidence into stupid session."

Maro's eyebrows moved.

Dask said, "That's annoyingly specific."

"Teacher."

"Who?"

"Hessa. Carrow."

Maro asked, "What was prior clean context?"

I answered carefully.

"Moving caster on practiced loop. Stationary bead. About one pace farther than prior near-range work. Two clean-enough out of ten counted farther attempts last session. External total one twenty-nine attempts, not successes."

Dask looked disappointed by sane accounting.

"And this?"

"First clean practical external effect I can remember outside deliberate practice in this body. Target was moving. I was seated. Distance maybe a pace and a half."

There.

Said aloud.

It landed after.

First.

Not first-life first.

This body's first.

Current life first.

Practical.

External.

No setup.

I looked at hand.

Nothing changed.

No glow.

No level announcement.

Mana felt normal.

Maybe slightly warm behind sternum.

Could be adrenaline.

Dask held marker.

"Again later?"

"Maybe. Not now."

Vessa had come close enough to hear.

"Does this affect trial?"

I asked before anyone else could.

"No."

Good.

"Magic isn't listed requirement," she said. "If hired and you use it on field equipment without instruction, it can affect trial retroactively by making me regret hiring you."

"Understood."

Dask looked personally robbed.

"I would've supervised."

"You were about to say do it again beside open yard traffic."

"Supervision can be spontaneous."

Sori called from table, "That sentence is why I keep permits."

Crew.

I drank water.

Inside, something had moved.

Small.

Not rank.

Not power in dramatic sense.

Transfer.

Hessa had been building a bridge from clean practice to ordinary movement one miserable counted attempt at a time.

Apparently one plank finally held.

I wanted to try again.

Badly.

I didn't.

That might have been more important than result.

Orin returned eating final bread crust.

Dask attacked his handwriting.

Orin's handwriting was beautiful.

Dask hated spacing.

Equal-opportunity misery.

My final trial piece was route pace.

Vessa had Orin do his separately while I waited, so no race.

Good.

Mine: personal load plus eight-pound case, longer yard circuit, one packed incline, gravel, timber step, dock lane, return.

No magic.

Explicitly.

Vessa said, "If you use magic to alter footing or load, I need to know before."

"I won't."

"Good."

I walked.

Not fast.

Faster than second route because geometry known now.

At incline, I needed six breaths.

Said six.

At dock lane, right hand started hot where crutch handle pressure concentrated.

Not injury.

I adjusted grip.

At gravel, no slip.

Finished.

Vessa checked time.

Then body.

"Hands hot. Shoulders worked. Right hip fine. No pain. I could continue after short rest."

"How long?"

I considered honestly.

"Five minutes before another similar circuit if you want same quality. Longer if repeated all morning."

"Good."

Orin did route later.

Faster.

Much faster.

He carried case by hand and moved like person with two free legs, which was offensively efficient.

He also missed red marker on first route because talking to Kel.

Vessa made him restart.

Second time clean.

He was strong.

Readable.

Good with numbers.

Less careful with instruction boundaries.

Maybe trainable.

I had no idea who Vessa wanted.

Good.

At midday she called both of us to shade.

"You both meet minimum read/write. Both can carry case through required yard geometry. Both can transfer cart and skiff with available crew procedure."

Orin exhaled.

I didn't.

Vessa continued.

"Orin, your pace is better. Your instruction retention is worse. You correct when caught. You talk while receiving directions. Stop."

Orin nodded rapidly.

Then realized.

Stopped nodding maybe.

"Greg. Your pace is slower but within my minimum on this yard route. You ask before improvising. You also have a mobility margin I cannot fully test here. North Glass Cut is rougher than yard. If hired, first section beyond maintained path gets conservative call. You do not prove anything to me by forcing it."

"Understood."

"Your skiff transfer requires crew assist. Acceptable. Your case carry requires crew strap configuration. Acceptable. Your personal harness has repair limitation. We keep case at eight and do not treat harness as rescue equipment."

"Yes."

"Oval stays in Merecross."

"Yes."

Orin looked between us.

"So who got it?"

Vessa looked at him.

"I haven't finished."

"Sorry."

"That."

He shut mouth.

I almost liked him more.

Vessa said, "I have one record-hand slot on posted crew. I also have Glasswake prep requiring two temporary record runners for three days before departure. Paid one copper per day plus meal."

New information.

Not invented to save both maybe.

Could be.

World had staffing needs.

"I want both of you for prep tomorrow through first Glasswake night. Same records rules. Different assignments. That gives me field-relevant observation under actual event pressure. One of you gets route slot after first night. If one declines prep, I decide from today."

There.

Conditional competition.

Adventure earned but not guaranteed.

Orin asked, "We get paid even if not chosen?"

"Yes."

"Then yes."

Vessa looked at me.

One copper per day.

Three days.

Meals.

More importantly actual crew work.

Glasswake.

Field decision after.

"Yes."

"Report second bell tomorrow. Not first. Sleep."

Sori handed each temporary slip.

I read mine before signing.

Of course.

Three days maximum.

One copper per completed prep day.

Meal.

No promise field assignment.

Can terminate for safety/instruction failure.

No exclusivity outside assigned hours.

Fine.

I signed.

Orin signed without reading all way.

Sori pulled it back.

"Read."

"I trust you."

"That's not flattering in records office. Read."

He read.

Good crew.

I collected oval from lockbox.

Still wrapped.

Still not coming if I went.

Outside Survey gate, Dask caught up.

"One thing."

"No."

"I didn't ask."

"You want me to move marker."

"Yes."

"No."

"Tomorrow?"

"Not during work."

"After?"

I thought.

Hessa would want controlled comparison.

Same target.

Known distance.

Wind note.

Body state.

Not random party trick.

"If we can set clean conditions and it doesn't interfere with work. Four attempts."

Dask brightened.

"Eight."

"Four."

"Six."

"You're negotiating my nervous system."

"Five."

"Four."

"Fine."

He was absolutely going to bring seven gauges.

"One target."

His face fell.

"You're cruel."

"Teacher."

I left.

At river rail, pre-pull light was stronger again.

Silver bands climbed bridge undersides.

Not full wake.

Soon.

I opened notebook.

TRIAL
- READ / COPY: PASS MINIMUM
- INSTRUCTION: MISSED ONE DETAIL FIRST REPEAT, CORRECT SECOND
- 8 LB CASE: WORKABLE WITH CREW STRAP / PERSONAL LOAD MOVED
- YARD PACE: SLOWER, WITHIN VESSA MINIMUM
- SKIFF TRANSFER: CREW ASSIST REQUIRED / ACCEPTABLE
- OVAL: LEAVE MERECROSS IF HIRED
- FIELD SLOT: NOT DECIDED

ORIN PELL
- OTHER CANDIDATE
- FASTER
- GOOD HANDWRITING
- TALKS THROUGH INSTRUCTIONS
- NOT ENEMY

Then magic.

I hesitated before writing because naming things made them feel larger.

EXTERNAL
- FIRST CLEAN PRACTICAL EFFECT OUTSIDE DELIBERATE TRAINING, CURRENT BODY
- SEATED
- MOVING BRASS DISK
- ~1.5 PACES
- NO SETUP
- ONE RESULT ONLY
- DID NOT REPEAT

Previous totals remained:

MINIMAL 59
SHAPING 53
EXTERNAL 129 ATTEMPTS

Not adding one.

It wasn't Hessa protocol.

Different evidence.

Better evidence in one way.

Worse in every controlled way.

Still mine.

Then work:

GLASSWAKE PREP RUNNER
3 DAYS MAX
1 C / COMPLETED DAY + MEAL
FIELD SLOT DECIDED AFTER FIRST NIGHT

Cash fourteen.

Vale twenty-three.

No money today.

No job yet.

But tomorrow I reported to River Survey as temporary crew.

Candidate.

For the road, maybe.

For something else too.

Small results fit through doors.

This one had.

# CHAPTER 386

## THE RUNNER

Temporary jobs had a smell.

Wet paper.

Borrowed pencils.

Food you did not choose.

And somebody saying, "You're with me," before you learned enough names to object.

Second bell.

River Survey yard.

Vessa pointed at Orin.

"Sori."

Then at me.

"Dask."

Dask Renn looked offended.

"Why me?"

"Because you asked for another pair of hands yesterday."

"I asked for competent hands."

"Then find out."

Fair.

Orin looked at me.

I looked at him.

"Good luck," he said.

"You too."

Sori snapped fingers.

"If either of you starts competing by doing extra work nobody assigned, I will personally record it as failure."

Orin closed mouth.

I had not opened mine.

Growth.

Vessa gave terms once.

Three prep days.

One copper each completed day plus meal.

Separate assignments.

Report changes.

Do not touch anything active unless assigned.

Do not improve labels.

Do not make route decisions because bored.

If separated by closure cordon, report to nearest marked crew station instead of inventing shortcut.

Orin repeated first.

He missed nearest marked crew station.

Caught himself.

"Sorry. Nearest marked crew station."

Vessa nodded.

Then me.

I repeated all.

Dask said, "Annoying."

"Thank you."

"Not compliment."

"Still taking it."

Vessa looked at Dask.

"Work."

We worked.

Glasswake prep did not begin with magic.

It began with tags.

Hundreds.

Dask's assignment was North Channel temporary gauge route.

We had twelve numbered marker housings, three portable sight boards, one black case of glass-safe chalk, two waterline slates, four cloth covers, and a small brass bell that Dask insisted was not decorative.

"If bell rings, stop writing."

"Why?"

"Because either I rang it or river did."

"River can ring your bell?"

"Not mine specifically. That would be rude."

He handed me stack of route slips.

"Copy station number. Time. Waterline mark. Light direction if visible. Do not write cause. Do not write prediction. If you don't see light, write none observed, not no light."

Good.

"Difference understood."

"Say difference."

Of course.

"No light claims absence. None observed claims I didn't observe it from this station/time."

Dask nodded.

"You may live."

We left yard.

North Channel route followed river upstream from center bridge through freight lanes I had not seen.

Merecross changed block by block.

North Market polished itself for visitors.

Freight district did not care.

Stone warehouses.

Chain lifts.

Fish scales in gutters.

Long wooden chutes from upper doors.

Men and women carrying loads too awkward for dignity.

Glasswake light moved underneath everything now.

Pale silver along wagon undersides.

Blue-white seams in puddles.

At certain angles, shadows had edges brighter than objects casting them.

Nobody stopped.

Workers had jobs.

So did I.

Station one.

Dask opened housing.

Mundane brass scale inside, etched water marks, narrow inset of smoky glass at center.

"Active?"

"Reactive register. Doesn't generate. Doesn't predict. Reads local return against fixed housing reference."

"How?"

"Later. Record now."

Good boundary.

Waterline one thumb below red notch.

Smoky inset showed pale diagonal thread.

Dask read angle with small sight fork.

"North-east bias, low."

I wrote exactly.

Station two.

No visible inset line from my angle.

Dask saw one after shading with hand.

I wrote what he dictated, not what I thought I had missed.

Station three.

Housing stuck.

Dask swore at screw for thirty seconds.

Then asked me to hold cover while he used tool.

I held cover.

Did not solve screw.

Temporary job success.

At station four, we met Orin and Sori crossing opposite direction.

Orin carried permit tube, two clipboards, and face of man learning paper could weigh thirty pounds spiritually.

"How's Dask?"

"Still alive."

Dask from housing: "Regrettably."

Sori said, "Orin has learned four different departments use same word `clear` for four different things."

Orin looked haunted.

"One means path open. One means permit accepted. One means no reflective contamination. One means handwriting readable."

"That last one seems ambitious."

Sori pointed pencil at me.

"Do not encourage him."

We passed.

No rivalry.

Just different suffering.

Station five sat near low freight landing.

This one mattered.

Dask opened housing.

Smoky inset line was not diagonal.

It was two lines.

One steady.

One faint and moving.

Dask froze.

Not fear.

Attention.

"Don't write yet."

I stopped.

He shaded inset.

Looked without fork.

Used fork.

Looked upriver.

Then downstream.

"Bell."

He handed me brass bell.

"Ring twice."

I rang.

Workers nearby did not panic.

They stepped away from painted river edge.

Two freight handlers covered reflective cargo with cloth.

Procedure.

Good.

Dask said, "Now record: station five, second bell plus thirty-two, primary north-east low, secondary variable west-to-east, intermittent. Observed after housing opened."

I wrote.

"Cause?"

"No."

"I know."

"Then why ask?"

"Conversation."

"Dangerous habit."

He closed housing.

"We wait one minute."

We waited.

Secondary line appeared on closed slit too.

Fainter.

Dask rang once himself.

Different signal apparently.

Crew station upstream answered one bell.

Communication.

He wrote small symbol on his sheet.

I did not copy because he had not told me to.

Dask noticed.

"Good."

There it was.

Actually earned.

"What does one bell mean?"

"Observation acknowledged. Two means local hold. Three means clear immediate edge and find supervisor. Four is not used because nobody remembers four under stress."

"Smart."

"It was five originally."

"Less smart."

"Exactly."

Station six required stairs.

Not many.

Nine stone down to lower quay.

Handrail right.

I took slow.

Dask waited without commentary.

Good.

At bottom, river was close enough I could smell cold mineral over fish rot.

Glasswake light under surface stronger.

Not full wake.

But threads had widened into thin sheets that flexed under current while seeming to travel wrong direction.

Beautiful.

I wanted to stare.

Dask said, "Work first."

I disliked him correctly.

Station six normal.

Seven missing cover strap.

I noted maintenance need only after Dask said yes.

Eight had waterline higher by half finger from morning reference slate.

Nine was where day stopped being paperwork.

Freight cart had parked partly over station housing.

Driver nowhere.

Dask stared.

"Of course."

"Move cart?"

"No."

Good.

"Find driver?"

"Yes."

We found driver two stalls away arguing over eel invoice.

Dask explained housing access.

Driver said five minutes.

Dask said now.

Driver said freight permit.

Dask said gauge route priority during pre-wake.

Driver said show permit.

Dask looked at me.

"Sori."

Of course.

I had runner feet now.

Crutches.

Whatever.

"Nearest marked crew station?"

Dask pointed blue flag one block upriver.

"Ask for route permit copy or Sori. Do not go market office unless station sends you."

I repeated.

Then went.

Running was not running.

Fast crutch pace through freight crowd.

Right-side harness light.

Shoulder bag stable.

Baret repair held.

Blue station occupied by Kel and Orin.

Interesting.

Orin held one end of narrow measuring line while Kel argued with mooring post.

"Need Sori or gauge route permit copy," I said.

Orin said, "Sori went permit office. Copy in my tube."

He dug.

Three sheets.

Wrong one first.

Caught before handing.

"No, that's center load. North Channel gauge route here."

He gave.

"Need original?"

"Copy acceptable?"

"Stamped duplicate."

"Good."

Kel said, "Why?"

I explained blocked housing, driver wants proof.

Kel grunted.

"Normal."

Excellent city.

Back.

Driver read permit.

Moved cart.

Station nine housing had no damage.

Work resumed.

No heroics.

At station ten, Orin caught us.

"Sori says I stay with you until she returns."

Dask looked offended at extra person.

"Can you be quiet?"

Orin considered.

"Probably."

"Bad answer."

"Then yes."

He lasted forty seconds.

"What does smoky glass actually do?"

Dask stopped.

I expected explosion.

Instead he looked pleased.

Technical people were predictable in one way.

Ask correct question and suffer lecture.

"Local reflective-return bias under current illumination and river response. It is not measuring light quantity. It is measuring relation between fixed reference and directional return. Think of a compass if compass cared where reflection behaved instead of north."

"So it tells where Glasswake is pushing?"

"No."

Orin looked wounded.

Dask continued.

"It tells what this housing registers here now. `Pushing` invents force mechanism. We map stations, compare, then people with broader models argue."

Orin nodded.

"So not prediction."

"Correct."

"Could enough stations predict?"

Dask's eye twitched.

"Other people get paid more to be wrong with larger maps."

I laughed.

Orin did too.

Dask almost did.

Crew becoming people.

Station ten line bright enough visible without shade.

Eleven brighter.

At eleven, something strange happened to me.

Not power-up.

Good.

I planted left crutch on stone.

Felt vibration.

Tiny.

Three pulses.

Pause.

Two.

I moved crutch.

Gone.

Put back same seam.

Three.

Pause.

Two.

"Dask."

He was reading housing.

"What?"

"Stone seam under left crutch. Repeating vibration. Three, pause, two."

He came over.

Put boot on seam.

Nothing.

Crouched, fingertips.

Waited.

"Again?"

I felt.

"Three. Pause. Two."

Orin put palm down.

"I don't feel anything."

Could be crutch transmitting better.

Could be me.

Could be magic sensitivity.

Do not jump.

Dask said, "Use other crutch."

I did.

Right crutch tip same seam.

Three.

Pause.

Two.

"Both."

He tapped seam with metal pencil cap.

Hollow-ish ring.

"Probably drain conduit vibration."

Probably.

"Record?"

Dask thought.

"Separate observation. Not gauge sheet. Station eleven adjacent stone seam, repeating tactile pulse reported through crutch tips, three-two pattern, not independently felt by boots/hands."

Orin said, "Could crutches amplify?"

"Yes."

"Could Greg?"

Dask looked at me.

Yesterday's brass marker.

"Maybe."

There.

Tiny scaling without claim.

My current body might be getting better at noticing magical or mechanical vibration through tools because crutches were effectively long contact probes.

Or just hollow conduit.

Both interesting.

Dask marked for drain cross-check.

We did not investigate.

Good discipline.

Station twelve sat at old north floodgate.

Largest housing.

Maro already there.

Black river-light case open.

Sori returned too.

Vessa leaned against railing reading two sheets at once.

Apparently assignments converged here.

Kel arrived behind with cart.

Temporary crew became crew-shaped.

Vessa asked Dask, "Anything?"

"Station five secondary variable return, acknowledged upstream. Eleven has tactile three-two report through Greg's crutch tips only. Need drain cross-check before pretending meaningful. Ten through twelve strengthening primary line."

"Records clean?"

Dask looked at my sheets.

"Mostly."

"Mostly?"

"He wrote `NE low` once."

I blinked.

Sori's head snapped.

"You abbreviated?"

"Dask said north-east low out loud at every prior station. I copied his exact short form from header legend."

Dask checked.

He had forgotten legend.

Sori smiled slowly.

"Oh, this is beautiful."

Dask looked at header.

"Accepted abbreviation."

"Say sorry."

"No."

"Say Greg was right."

"Excessive."

Vessa said, "Records clean?"

Dask sighed.

"Yes."

Tiny competence win.

No fireworks.

Good.

Orin's sheets got reviewed next.

His handwriting genuinely excellent.

Sori found one missing permit-source line.

Orin swore softly.

"I knew that."

"Knowing is not recording."

"I know."

"You just did it again."

He rubbed face.

Not incompetent.

Pressure exposing habit.

Vessa gave him back sheet to correct with timestamp and late-entry mark, not pretend original.

Good system.

Then floodgate did something.

Low stone arch under us gave one deep sound.

Not bell.

Not crack.

Like somebody plucked bridge cable underwater.

Everyone stopped.

Maro shut case.

Kel grabbed cart brake.

Sori capped ink.

Dask raised hand.

Nobody needed instruction.

Second sound.

Higher.

River surface beneath gate flashed white from below.

Not explosion.

Sheet of light.

For half second, underside of arch became bright enough every old repair line showed black.

I saw three things immediately.

One, everyone local moved back from rail.

Two, Orin moved too but almost left clipboard on rail.

Three, clipboard started sliding because rail had begun vibrating.

Yesterday I might have used magic.

Today I did not.

I hooked clipboard edge with crutch cuff and pulled it inward.

Mundane first.

Orin grabbed.

"Thanks."

"Buy me kingdom."

"Later."

Vessa pointed inland.

"Blue line."

We moved to painted blue safety line six paces from water.

No argument.

Third sound did not come.

Instead river under gate lit in broad pale fan stretching downstream.

People on opposite bank cheered.

Festival response.

Maro swore.

"Too early?" I asked.

"Not wake."

Good.

"What then?"

"Gate sheet response. Bigger than yesterday."

Dask said, "Station twelve?"

Maro: "Don't open housing until light settles."

Dask hated but complied.

Vessa assigned.

"Sori, time and witnesses. Orin, crowd side, tell freight crew blue line remains closed until release. Greg, with Dask when Maro clears. Kel, cart stays."

There.

Real work.

Orin went crowd side.

People asked questions.

He did not invent answers.

I heard him repeat, "Temporary hold. No cause statement yet. Stay behind blue."

Good.

Twenty breaths.

Thirty.

Light fan narrowed.

Maro watched black glass strip in handheld frame.

"Now."

Dask opened station twelve.

Smoky inset was brilliant.

Two lines again.

No.

Three.

Primary steady north-east.

Secondary west-east.

Third almost vertical.

Dask breathed, "Oh, fuck you."

Scientific phrase.

"Write," he said.

I wrote exact dictation.

Time.

Three returns.

Relative brightness.

Third intermittent.

No mechanism.

No prediction.

Then third line vanished.

"Mark duration from open to disappearance."

I had time.

Because I had started when housing opened.

"Forty-one breaths approximate, based on spoken count?"

Dask looked.

"You counted?"

"Started because yesterday you cared duration."

He stared.

"Approximate because breathing variable."

"Yes."

"Write approximate forty-one breaths observer-count, not instrument time."

I did.

That was probably best moment of day.

Not magic.

Pattern recognition.

Old-life field brain meeting current job.

Vessa heard.

No praise.

But later she asked for copy herself.

That counted.

At meal break, crew sat on overturned cases behind floodgate office.

Stew in metal bowls.

Orin ate like apology.

Dask complained bowl rims altered spoon angle.

Sori told him nobody had ever needed that sentence.

Kel said, "He's said worse."

Maro ate quietly.

Vessa stood until Sori told her sitting was allowed by law.

She sat.

Temporary ensemble.

I liked it.

Dangerous.

Orin asked me, "You actually fought monsters?"

I looked at him.

"Why?"

"Your face yesterday when Vessa listed no monster warning."

I had face problem.

"First life."

Silence changed.

Not dramatic.

They knew reincarnation existed? Probably context established in world maybe. Better not overexplain.

Dask said, "Class?"

"S-class eventually."

Orin stopped spoon.

"Bullshit."

Fair.

"Yes."

"Yes bullshit?"

"Yes fair response."

Maro looked at me differently.

Not awe.

Recalculation.

Vessa said, "Current body Bronze."

"Yes."

"Current route trial based current body."

"Yes."

"Then old rank is context, not credential."

"Correct."

Good.

Orin leaned forward.

"But you were S-class."

"At fifty-something."

"Fifty-nine," I said after thinking.

"And now you're nineteen."

"Unfortunately."

Dask said, "That explains your annoying refusal to repeat external effect yesterday."

"Hessa explains that."

"Who?"

"Teacher."

Sori smiled.

"You have teacher."

"Everybody should."

Vessa nodded once.

Important.

No hero worship.

But this was scaling too, in another direction.

Crew now understood Greg's competence came from somewhere, while current limitations remained real.

Orin said, "I would've repeated it."

"I know."

"How?"

"You talk during instructions."

He threw bread crust at me.

Missed.

Dask said, "Poor trajectory."

Crew laughed.

There.

Belonging starting.

Afternoon split again.

Orin with Kel freight-route copies.

Me with Sori this time because Dask had station reports to compile.

Sori's work was less magical and more dangerous in another way.

Permit reconciliation.

Temporary Glasswake closures had changed three market routes, two freight windows, and one river-light access boundary.

Every paper wanted same truth.

They did not have it.

We walked desk to desk collecting stamped changes.

Sori made me read each back.

At third office, clerk tried giving verbal update without stamp.

I almost accepted because information sounded clear.

Sori waited.

I caught.

"Need stamped change or note source as verbal pending confirmation."

Clerk sighed.

"Stamp is upstairs."

"Then verbal pending?"

Sori said, "Your choice if your sheet."

I wrote verbal pending, clerk name/time, not operational authority.

Good.

Later stamped copy arrived and superseded.

Small work.

Real.

Near last bell, Vessa called both candidates yard.

One day complete.

She did not rank us.

Cruel.

"Tomorrow first bell, not second. Glass response accelerating. Prep day two starts earlier."

Orin repeated.

I repeated.

"Pay."

Sori handed each one copper.

Fourteen to fifteen.

Meal already eaten.

Vessa added, "No field decision today."

Orin said, "Expected."

I said nothing.

"Greg."

"Yes?"

"Do not count silence as virtue."

Hessa had traveled spiritually.

"Understood."

Dask came over with station eleven note.

"Drain cross-check."

"And?"

"There is old return conduit under seam. Mechanical pulse from north sluice timing relay is three-two today because one gate offline."

Mundane-ish answer.

"So not me."

"Probably not you. Crutch tip transmitted vibration efficiently."

I felt relief and disappointment.

Both stupid.

Dask continued.

"Still useful observation. Boots missed it."

There.

Competence without magical inflation.

"Good."

He narrowed eyes.

"Don't say it like her."

"Like who?"

"Your teacher. I haven't met her and already dislike influence."

Excellent.

At inn, I wrote:

PREP DAY 1 COMPLETE +1 C
CASH 15 C
VALE 23 C

STATION 5
- SECONDARY VARIABLE RETURN
- RECORDED / ACKNOWLEDGED

STATION 11
- CRUTCH VIBRATION WAS OLD RETURN CONDUIT / 3-2 RELAY PATTERN WITH ONE GATE OFFLINE
- NOT MAGIC EVIDENCE
- CRUTCH TIPS USEFUL CONTACT PROBES MAYBE

STATION 12
- GATE SHEET RESPONSE
- 3 LINES TEMPORARILY
- THIRD ~41 BREATHS OBSERVER COUNT, APPROX
- NO CAUSE / NO PREDICTION

Then:

ORIN
- FAST
- GOOD HANDWRITING
- STILL TALKS DURING INSTRUCTIONS
- DID NOT INVENT ANSWERS AT BLUE LINE

Then crew.

VESSA
MARO
SORI
KEL
DASK
ORIN
ME, TEMPORARILY

I looked at temporarily.

Tomorrow first bell.

Glasswake was getting brighter.

Field slot still one.

But today I had not been candidate all day.

Mostly I had been running papers, bells, permits, observations, and one very ordinary stamped copy between people who needed them.

Runner.

Not glamorous.

Useful.

That was becoming dangerous word.

# CHAPTER 387

## THE CREWMATE

Orin hated boats.

This improved him immediately.

"I don't hate boats," he said while gripping dock post hard enough to confess otherwise.

"You look peaceful."

"I hate unsecured transitions onto boats."

Kel, already in skiff, said, "That's boats."

"No, that's boarding."

"Then you love them after boarding?"

"I haven't tested enough."

Dask, from dock, said, "Good answer."

Sori said, "Do not encourage him."

First bell had barely finished.

Glasswake prep day two.

Vessa had changed assignments before breakfast ended.

Greg and Orin together.

Excellent.

Not because teamwork pleasant.

Because now one of us could make mistakes in front of other.

Different pressure.

Task looked simple.

Move six temporary river-light reference boards from north storage to two floating work platforms upstream of old floodgate.

Record serial, placement, time, assigned angle mark.

Do not adjust anything after Maro sets it.

Do not touch mirror face.

Do not improvise around mooring lines.

Kel handled boat.

Maro set boards.

Dask read.

Sori kept custody record.

Greg and Orin moved paperwork, covers, and mundane parts.

Vessa floated between both platforms and shore because apparently leadership meant never getting comfortable.

We repeated instructions.

Orin got all of them.

He looked at me after.

"I can learn."

"Terrifying."

"You're threatened."

"Deeply."

Kel slapped skiff side.

"Board."

Orin stared.

"You first."

"Coward."

"Demonstration learner."

I went first.

Crutches to socket.

Dock post left hand.

Kel forearm right.

Right foot down.

Boat moved.

I froze.

Kel waited.

Then settled me onto bench.

Better than trial.

Not elegant.

Good enough.

Orin followed.

Boat moved less for him.

He still made face.

"Love it?" I asked.

"Shut up."

Crew laughed.

Social belonging apparently required mild cruelty.

Fine.

River brighter today.

Not pre-pull threads anymore.

Broad silver sheets moved under surface in long slow planes, sometimes crossing current direction, sometimes folding into each other and vanishing.

Buildings along bank wore pale light underneath eaves.

Metal edges shone from below.

Every shadow near water had second shadow, brighter and wrong.

Merecross looked upside down without actually changing orientation.

I loved it.

Dangerous.

Maro noticed stare.

"Look now. Work later."

"Those are compatible."

"Not always."

Fair.

First platform floated thirty paces offshore, chained to two stone dolphins.

Not animal.

Mooring posts shaped like animal because people couldn't resist confusing language.

Kel brought skiff alongside.

Platform deck rose six inches above boat gunwale.

Transfer easier than dock because handrail fixed and platform movement matched river more closely.

Still movement.

I waited until Kel said go.

Orin climbed after me.

Maro pointed first reference board location.

Board itself was narrow wood frame with dull black backing and small inset mirror-glass square.

Heavy enough that Orin carried it.

I carried cover straps and serial sheet.

No competition there.

Eight-pound case yesterday had already established reality.

Board one: N-17.

Maro set legs into marked sockets.

Dask read angle.

"Three marks riverward, one low."

Sori said, "Record exact."

Orin wrote.

Beautiful handwriting.

Bastard.

I read back serial.

N-17.

Time.

Angle.

Maro uncovered glass.

Nothing dramatic.

Pale line formed and held.

Board two N-18 went downstream corner.

Board three refused flat footing because one platform plank had swollen.

Maro did not shim randomly.

He called Vessa.

Vessa inspected socket.

"Use alternate mark."

Sori checked work order.

"Alternate mark B authorized if primary seat uneven."

There.

System had branch.

Not hero.

Orin moved board.

I copied reason:

PRIMARY SEAT UNEVEN / ALT B PER WORK ORDER

Dask peered over.

"Good."

"You sound surprised."

"I sound accurate."

"Worse."

Second platform was farther upstream.

Kel took us back into skiff.

This time Orin boarded first.

Growth.

He still hated it.

Also growth.

Halfway across, Glasswake did something new.

River surface ahead turned dark.

Not shadow.

A long oval patch maybe twenty feet across lost reflection entirely.

Cloud, buildings, silver pre-pull, all gone.

Black water.

Kel slowed immediately.

Maro said, "Hold."

We held.

Patch moved sideways across current.

Slow.

Then bright rim formed around edge.

Dask leaned too far.

Sori grabbed back of coat.

"No."

"I was observing."

"Observe with spine inside boat."

"Restriction unnecessary."

"Keep testing."

Kel muttered, "Married people fight quieter."

"We are not married," Sori and Dask said together.

Kel smiled.

I stored that.

Orin whispered, "Are they?"

"Apparently not."

Maro said, "Black pocket."

"Normal?" Orin asked.

"Known. Not common this early."

"Danger?"

"Navigation problem if large. Depth looks wrong. Light cues disappear. Some old mirror pockets take return instead of throwing it."

"Cause?"

Maro stared at him.

Orin sighed.

"Right. Not asking cause."

Learning.

Kel waited until dark oval passed downstream.

Then continued.

Second platform already had two city workers tightening lantern rail clamps.

One was woman with red scarf and strong opinion about Orin standing in her rope lane.

"Move."

He moved.

"Other side."

He moved again.

"No, not there. That's my tool zone."

Orin looked around.

"Where do you want me?"

"There."

She pointed exactly where he started.

He looked at me.

I looked away because friendship had limits.

We set boards four and five.

Board six had problem.

Serial on wood frame read N-22.

Custody sheet read N-23.

Sori stopped everything.

"Nobody installs."

Dask checked etched plate.

N-22.

Maro checked cover tag.

N-23.

Two identifiers disagreed.

Orin said, "Probably cover swapped."

Sori said, "Probably is decorative word."

"I know."

"Then don't use it operationally."

He nodded.

No defensive speech.

Good.

Vessa arrived.

"Options?"

Sori: "Return board and cover to storage for custody correction, use reserve if available."

Dask: "Board can still be measured physically."

Sori: "Not installed under wrong custody."

Dask: "I didn't say installed."

"Your tone did."

Vessa looked at Greg and Orin.

"You two. Reserve retrieval. Blue station north dock. Bring stamped reserve release, not verbal."

There.

Runner again.

But together.

Kel took us shore.

North dock busy now.

Glasswake crowd beginning early even before first night.

Tourists maybe.

Locals maybe.

Hard to tell until somebody paid too much for bridge model.

We moved fast through dock lane.

Orin faster naturally.

He got ten paces ahead.

Then stopped.

Waited.

No comment.

Good.

At blue station clerk found reserve board N-24.

"Take it."

Orin reached.

I said, "Stamp."

He stopped.

Clerk frowned.

"You're River Survey."

"Need stamped reserve release."

"Vessa knows."

"Still need stamp."

Clerk swore softly, found stamp.

Orin grinned at me.

"I remembered."

"After my word."

"Shared memory. Teamwork."

"Fraud."

We carried N-24 together because board awkward, not particularly heavy.

Orin took front.

I took rear with one hand impossible because crutches.

No.

We stopped immediately.

"I can't carry board and both crutches."

Orin said, "I can solo."

"It's long."

"I can solo awkward."

He proved.

Board under arm, angled vertical.

I carried paperwork and cleared people.

Not glamorous.

Worked.

On return, crowd thickened near north viewing rail.

Pale light under river had become bright enough people gasped whenever broad sheet rolled beneath center bridge.

Then bell rang two.

Not Dask handheld.

City bell.

Two low.

People around us reacted.

Dema's voice somewhere ahead:

"BACK FROM RAIL. TWO STEPS. DON'T ARGUE WITH ME TODAY."

Of course.

Market licensing apparently became crowd control when river misbehaved.

We reached edge of knot.

Dema saw us.

"Survey?"

"Reserve board," I said.

"Route blocked for one minute."

Orin asked, "Why?"

Dema pointed river.

A black pocket had formed directly under viewing rail.

Larger than one from boat.

Maybe forty feet long.

Bright rim climbed stone embankment.

Not physically climbed.

Light did.

Silver edge crawled upward over wet blocks toward rail supports.

People backed.

One man did not.

He leaned farther for view.

Dema grabbed coat and yanked.

"Sir."

"I paid for terrace."

"Congratulations. Back."

"Is it dangerous?"

"I don't know yet. Back."

He resisted just enough to become idiot.

Orin stepped forward.

Not threatening.

"She said back."

Man looked at him.

Orin was broad.

Helpful.

Man backed.

Dema looked at Orin.

"Good face."

"Thanks?"

"Don't enjoy it."

We waited.

Silver rim reached first iron rail post.

Post rang.

Single clear note.

Then next post rang.

Then third.

One by one upstream.

Not bell system.

Metal responding.

Dema called runner.

"River-light and structures. North terrace. Sequential rail tone."

Exact.

Greg watched sequence.

Post one.

Two.

Three.

Four.

Timing not equal.

Intervals shortened.

First about six breaths.

Then five.

Then three.

I started count without thinking.

No magic.

Just habit.

At fifth post, tone doubled.

Two notes close together.

Then light stopped climbing.

Held.

Dema asked, "Did anyone touch rail?"

Crowd voices.

No clear.

I said, "I didn't see contact from post one through five. My angle partial on first."

Orin said, "Same. I saw two through five. Nobody touched."

Good independent witness.

Runner arrived with Maro behind him somehow faster than bureaucracy.

Maro took description.

Greg gave sequence and approximate intervals.

Orin independently gave visible-contact range.

Dema gave crowd/control context.

Nobody invented cause.

Maro looked rail.

"Old return bars inside stone tie into posts. Could be conductive response. Could be something else. Keep people off rail until structures checks."

Dema said, "Already doing that."

"Good."

Orin whispered, "Everyone here likes saying good when they mean thank you."

"Good observation."

He glared.

Route opened around terrace rear.

We continued.

At platform, reserve board N-24 installed.

Sori checked stamp.

"Correct."

Orin said, "Greg remembered."

I looked at him.

He shrugged.

No stealing credit.

Interesting.

Vessa asked what delayed us.

We gave bounded report.

She listened.

Then asked, "Which of you initiated contact with terrace response?"

"Neither," we said.

"Good."

There it was again.

Afternoon work shifted from platforms to shore-line mirror covers.

Glass intensity now made uncovered idle faces throw stray bands into alleys.

Crew had to cover or angle nonessential panels before first night.

Not all magical objects.

Some literal mirrors.

Some treated river-glass.

Maro handled classification.

Greg and Orin carried covers and tags.

At third site, warehouse owner refused cover.

"That panel advertises during wake."

Sori checked permit.

"Your active display window starts first-night third bell. Until then temporary glare restriction applies."

"I've done this twelve years."

"Then you've signed twelve permits."

He hated her.

Understandable.

Orin nearly laughed.

I did not.

Professional.

Mostly.

Owner pointed at Greg.

"Why is he here?"

"Temporary Survey record runner," Sori said.

"He looks like theatre."

I stared.

How.

Maybe coat.

"I contain multitudes."

Sori pinched bridge of nose.

Owner laughed despite himself.

Panel covered.

Work moved.

Late afternoon, Vessa sent Greg and Orin with Kel to old west service stairs carrying empty tag cases and two cloth rolls.

No record case.

Physical read.

Stairs descended to maintenance landing above river.

Twenty-two stone steps.

Then narrow flat.

Then eleven more.

Greg took time.

Orin carried cloth.

Kel watched both without comment.

At lower landing, Glasswake light came through gaps between quay stones in thin vertical knives.

Beautiful.

Also made footing weird because edges looked displaced.

Greg stopped.

"Visual distortion."

Kel said, "Yes."

Orin tested next tread with boot.

"Edge is where it looks?"

"Mostly," Kel said.

"Mostly bad word."

"Use wall."

Greg used crutch tip before right foot.

One step appeared half inch higher from light than actual.

Not dangerous once tested.

But repeated.

Old-life terrain judgment kicked in.

"Don't trust lit edge. Trust contact."

Kel nodded.

Orin copied, tapping toe first.

We descended slower.

No one failed.

At bottom, Kel said, "Field route has worse visual nonsense at Glass Cut if wake residue high."

There.

Real preview.

"Worse physical?" Greg asked.

"Different. More sharp material, less stairs."

Orin said, "I hate both equally."

Kel said, "Healthy."

We fixed cloth covers over two unused service mirrors under Kel direction.

On way up, Greg needed rest after first twenty-two.

"Eight breaths," I said.

Then after four:

"Actually twelve."

Orin leaned wall.

"I'm taking twelve too."

"You don't need twelve."

"I need solidarity."

"You need excuse."

"Both can happen."

Kel waited.

No judgment.

At top, I was tired.

Not ruined.

Useful data.

Orin looked fresher.

Also useful data.

No resentment.

Maybe small.

Human.

Back at Survey Office, Vessa collected day summaries.

She asked Orin first.

"Greg?"

Orin thought.

"Slower. Notices record problems. Doesn't pretend he can carry things he can't. Annoying."

"Useful annoying?"

"Usually."

My turn.

"Orin?"

"Faster. Better handwriting. Better today at not talking through instructions. Useful face for stubborn tourists. Still hates boats."

"I do not hate boats," Orin said.

Vessa ignored.

"Either of you cover for other?"

"No," we said.

Vessa nodded.

"Good."

Then she looked at me.

"West stairs?"

"Completed. Needed twelve breaths after first twenty-two on return. Could continue after. Visual edge distortion manageable with contact checking."

"Orin?"

"No physical issue. Same visual distortion."

Vessa wrote.

No praise.

Fine.

Sori paid us.

One copper each.

Fifteen to sixteen.

Meal had been midday bread, smoked fish, hard cheese.

Dask complained cheese geometry made crumbs enter field sheets.

Nobody cared.

Before dismissal Maro said first Glasswake night likely **tomorrow night**, not day after, if current brightening continued.

Not certain.

Operational planning moved as if possible.

That meant final prep day and first-night observation might compress together.

Field-choice sooner.

Departure potentially morning after.

Adventure suddenly calendar instead of idea.

At inn, I wrote:

CASH: 16 C
VALE: 23 C

PREP DAY 2 COMPLETE

ORIN
- FASTER
- GOOD HAND
- LESS BAD AT LISTENING TODAY
- HATES BOATS
- DID NOT TAKE CREDIT FOR STAMP

Then:

NORTH TERRACE
- BLACK POCKET
- RAIL POSTS RANG IN SEQUENCE
- INTERVALS SHORTENED APPROX 6 / 5 / 3 BREATHS BEFORE DOUBLE TONE
- NO OBSERVED TOUCH POSTS 2-5 (ORIN ALSO)
- MARO: OLD RETURN BARS MAY TIE INTO POSTS, NOT CAUSE CLAIM

Then west stairs.

TWELVE BREATHS AFTER 22 UP.
NOT FAILURE.
DATA.

I stared at field slot line.

Still undecided.

Strangely, I did not want Orin to fail anymore.

That complicated things.

Crewmate.

Candidate too.

Apparently both fit.

# CHAPTER 388

## THE WATCHMAN

Glasswake arrived while I was eating an egg.

Rude.

Not because egg mattered.

Egg was mediocre.

But I had been told first night likely tonight, which implied night.

Merecross disagreed with scheduling.

The spoon in my bowl lit from underneath.

Not reflected light.

Underneath.

Thin white line ran along metal handle, reached my fingers, and stopped.

I stopped too.

Innkeeper looked over from counter.

"There."

"There what?"

"First wake."

Outside, three bells rang from river.

Not alarm pattern I knew.

Then another tower answered.

Then another.

People in common room stood at once.

Nobody screamed.

One man finished coffee first.

Priorities.

I swallowed egg.

Harness.

Bag.

Crutches.

Survey Office.

By time I reached street, Merecross had changed.

Not brighter.

Brightness was wrong word.

More directions of light existed.

Silver moved beneath roof tiles.

Blue-white lines traced wet mortar between stones.

Windows reflected river even when river was behind them.

A woman's shadow stretched east while she walked west.

Then corrected itself.

She did not notice.

I did.

Excellent city.

Terrible city.

River bells rang again.

Survey yard already moving.

Vessa saw me enter.

"You're late."

"Wake arrived during egg."

"Known operational hazard."

Orin came behind me breathing hard.

"Mine was porridge."

"Worse."

"Thank you."

Vessa pointed at table.

"Eat enough?"

"Yes."

"Greg?"

"Yes."

"Good. Today is not candidate theater. You're both temporary crew until I say otherwise. If I see either of you performing for job, I will solve problem by hiring neither."

Fair.

Maro entered carrying black case.

His face had changed.

Not fear.

Attention.

"First-night threshold crossed at north gauge six minutes before bells. River-light response is broad. Black pockets already forming west channel."

Dask appeared behind him.

"Seven minutes if you use first stable line."

"Six if you use first repeat."

"Which is why we need definitions."

Sori handed both papers.

"You can fight after custody."

Kel came from stable yard.

"North platform two broke outer tie. Still held inner. I pulled it."

Vessa nodded.

"No floating platforms tonight. Shore stations only until Maro clears. Dask, north gauges. Sori, closures and custody. Kel, transport. Maro, light classification. Orin, with Sori first. Greg, with me."

That was new.

Vessa looked at both of us.

"First-night rules. If light removes edge certainty, stop. If crowd crosses blue, call it before fixing paper. If instruction conflicts with what you physically see, repeat conflict aloud. If separated, marked station. If you don't know, say don't know."

We repeated.

Orin clean.

Me clean.

Then city began.

Not disaster.

Festival.

That was stranger.

People poured toward river while workers tried to keep routes open.

Lantern frames from North Market were lit now, but flame inside them looked almost ordinary compared with river.

The water had become layered glass.

White sheets moved downstream.

Pale green lines crossed upstream beneath them.

Deep under both, something amber pulsed slowly enough I could count between appearances.

Seven breaths.

Eight.

Seven.

Not regular enough.

Do not make pattern because brain wanted one.

Children shouted every time amber returned.

Vendors shouted louder.

Somebody sold little black cloth squares for viewing bright water.

Capitalism survived magic.

Vessa walked fast.

I worked to stay with her.

Not impossible.

Not comfortable.

We reached North Terrace.

Dema had half railing closed.

"Your river is misbehaving," I told her.

"My river?"

"You work licensing."

"Then your theatre owns every drunk."

Fair.

Black pocket under terrace had returned larger.

Maybe thirty feet across now.

No reflection.

No visible depth.

Looked like someone cut hole in world and filled it with black paint.

People loved it.

Of course.

Blue crowd line was three feet back from rail.

People kept leaning over it.

Dema kept moving them.

Vessa said, "Greg, west approach. Keep lane open. Record any rail tone sequence if it returns. Do not touch rail."

"Understood."

"Dema is local authority here."

"Unfortunately," Dema said.

Vessa left.

I became watchman.

Not glamorous.

Mostly telling people their bodies were wider than they believed.

"Keep west lane open."

"I'm standing."

"In lane."

"I'm not blocking anyone."

Three people squeezed around him.

"Evidence disagrees."

He moved.

Dema heard.

"Don't get funny. They argue longer if entertained."

"Cruel."

"Efficient."

Orin passed with Sori carrying permit tube.

He saw me.

"Promotion?"

"Wall."

"Prestigious."

Then rail rang.

One post.

Two.

Three.

I started breath count.

Four breaths.

Next.

Four.

Next came almost immediately, maybe two.

Then two posts rang together.

Crowd cheered.

Idiots.

Black pocket rim climbed embankment.

"Back behind blue," Dema said.

People moved mostly.

One boy ducked under line for better look.

His father grabbed shirt before I did anything.

Good father.

Then bright rim reached first rail support.

Every iron post hummed at once.

Not loud.

Felt in teeth.

I stepped back.

Crutch tips vibrated.

Different from conduit yesterday.

No pattern claim.

Dema looked at me.

"Three bells?"

"Not yet. Rail response only."

She nodded.

Maro arrived running.

Actually running.

First time I had seen him do anything quickly.

"Clear rail edge. Full terrace west half."

Dema did not ask why.

"West half clear! Move behind second blue! Now!"

Different voice.

No argument register.

People moved.

Mostly.

One vendor said, "My table's inside second blue."

Dema said, "Then your table is having private festival. Move."

He moved.

Maro crouched nowhere near rail and watched black pocket.

"Greg, tones?"

I gave sequence.

"Four, four, about two, then paired. Then all-post hum when rim touched first support. Observer breath count."

"Contact?"

"No observed person contact. Crowd was behind blue by all-post hum. Earlier first-post sightline partial."

"Good."

He wrote.

Not praise.

Useful.

Then black pocket collapsed.

Not exploded.

Collapsed.

Dark surface narrowed from both sides and vanished.

River light surged into space it left.

North Terrace became white.

For maybe one heartbeat every wet edge shone.

Stone seams.

Rail bolts.

People's eyes.

My crutch shafts.

The oval in my bag went cold.

There.

I froze.

Not because dramatic.

Because instruction conflict.

Artifact unactivated.

Unexpected physical change.

"Dema."

"What?"

"My declared artifact just changed temperature."

She stared.

"The oval?"

She knew from yesterday? Maybe crew gossip.

"Yes."

"Don't touch it."

"Wasn't."

Maro heard.

His head turned.

"Where?"

"Shoulder bag. Wrapped."

"Cold how?"

"Felt through bag against side. Sudden."

"Any movement?"

"None felt."

"Sound?"

"No."

"Leave it."

"Yes."

No convenient activation.

No prophecy.

Just cold.

Maro marked time.

"We'll compare after shift."

Good.

Then somebody at east end shouted.

Not danger.

Orin.

"Sori! Your closure copy is wrong!"

Sori stopped.

"Explain."

"It says terrace west half reopened after pocket collapse. That's old instruction from yesterday's template. Vessa said full west half stays clear until structures check tonight."

Sori looked sheet.

Then swore.

Actual swear.

"Fuck."

Excellent.

She tore copy corner, marked void, pulled replacement.

"Who else has this?"

"North station, market bell desk, maybe west stair runner."

"Greg!"

I looked.

"Marked station first," Dema said before Sori could send me into crowd.

Sori nodded.

"Correct. Greg, nearest blue station. Tell them closure sheet batch mark K-four is void pending corrected copy. Do not carry verbal reopening. Get acknowledgment mark."

"K-four void. No verbal reopening. Acknowledgment mark."

I went.

Fast.

Merecross at first Glasswake was worst possible place to move quickly.

Everyone stopped in middle of everything.

People looked up.

Down.

At river.

At windows.

At each other reflected in windows.

I used crutches like traffic argument.

"Lane. Sorry. Lane. Survey. Not important enough to die for, important enough to move."

A woman laughed and moved.

Blue station had clerk I didn't know.

"Batch K-four closure sheet void pending corrected copy. No verbal reopening. Need acknowledgment mark."

"Authority?"

"Sori Vale, River Survey custody. North Terrace west-half closure."

He checked board.

"K-four. Yes."

Stamped acknowledgment slip.

I returned.

Not heroic.

Breathing hard.

Sori took slip.

"Good. West stair runner already intercepted. Market bell desk?"

Orin said, "I'll take."

He did.

There.

No competition.

Work.

First night deepened.

By third bell the city looked impossible.

Glasswake proper.

River no longer reflected buildings consistently.

Sometimes it reflected sky.

Sometimes underside of bridges.

Once I looked down and saw a row of windows that did not belong to any building near us.

Maro told me not to stare.

"Old reflection?"

"Maybe."

"From where?"

"If I knew, I would have used more useful word than maybe."

Fair.

Street puddles became little traitors.

Some showed normal boots.

Some showed river light.

One showed stars.

Cloudy afternoon.

I stepped around that one.

Dema saw.

"It's shallow."

"I know."

"Then why?"

"I dislike puddle with ambitions."

She stepped through.

Normal splash.

"Coward."

"Alive coward."

"Barely."

We worked.

Hours.

That was part stories skipped.

Beautiful magical festival arrived and then someone still needed replacement cord at station seven.

Someone needed meal count.

A vendor's permit said one reflective panel and he had three.

A child lost shoe.

Kel found horse refusing one alley because light ran backward along drain. He took different alley rather than argue with horse.

Dask recorded seven separate return changes and complained two were aesthetically redundant.

Sori told him phenomena did not owe him variety.

Orin got yelled at by old woman because temporary closure blocked her usual fish stall route.

He listened for entire complaint.

Then walked her to alternate lane himself.

Good.

Annoying.

Good.

My leg began hurting around fifth bell.

Not injury.

Work.

Socket pressure.

Shoulders tired.

Hands sore.

I told Vessa.

"Need what?"

"Ten minutes seated. Then can continue."

"Take fifteen. Check skin."

No ceremony.

I sat behind station shed.

Removed left setup enough to check.

Redness, expected.

No open skin.

No hot spot I distrusted.

Fifteen minutes.

Orin appeared halfway through carrying two bowls.

"Vessa said eat."

"I already ate."

"That was morning. Time has continued."

Damn.

He handed bowl.

"How are you?"

"Fine."

He stared.

I corrected.

"Tired. Skin intact. Fifteen-minute rest."

"Better."

"You?"

"My feet hurt. Boats remain criminal. Sori has made me rewrite same custody transfer twice because I wrote 'north desk' and apparently there are three north desks."

"How dare reality have specificity."

"I know."

We ate.

He looked toward river.

Silver-white light pulsed through alley mouth.

"If you get slot," he said, "I think I hate you."

There.

Not joke exactly.

"Reasonable."

"No, I mean..."

He rubbed face.

Messy speech.

"I need work. I actually need it. Not adventure. My sister's rent went up. I told Vessa that on application because there was line for obligations. And I know that's not your problem. I know she can't hire based on whose life is sadder because then we'd all start bringing dead grandmothers. Mine are both dead, by way, so I could compete."

"Strong portfolio."

"Exactly. But if you get it, I will be happy because you're good and angry because I wanted money and then probably ashamed I was angry, which is inefficient."

I looked at him.

"If you get it, I'll be angry too."

"Really?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"Also happy."

"Less good."

"Can't help."

He ate.

Then said, "I think you're better at field record."

I did not answer immediately.

"You're faster."

"That's not what I said."

"No."

"And you know when not to write thing. I keep wanting page complete. Sori says blank with reason is sometimes more correct than filled with assumption. I hate that."

"You'll learn."

"Maybe."

"You learned listening in two days."

"Fuck you."

"See? Concise."

He laughed.

Then Vessa called us.

Rest over.

Night came properly sometime after sixth bell.

Hard to tell because city had been glowing for hours.

Glasswake became beautiful enough to make work difficult.

Every bridge looked doubled.

One real above water.

One pale below, offset sideways like river remembered bridge somewhere else.

Lanterns along market turned gold, but river answered silver.

Black pockets drifted through brightness like holes in cloth.

People sang from South Quay.

Not organized.

Different songs overlapping.

Someone beat drum.

Children carried nonreflective paper masks with eye slits.

Festival finally caught up with phenomenon.

I understood why Lyssa remembered it fourteen years later.

I wanted her here.

That feeling sat beside wanting to go farther.

Both fit.

Annoying.

Final assignment put both candidates at old floodgate with whole crew.

No more running routes.

Watch.

Record.

Hold boundaries.

Dask had station twelve open under black hood.

Maro watched water.

Kel kept people off service landing.

Sori had three clocks, somehow.

Vessa stood center.

"We expect strongest return around next bell if current trend holds," Maro said.

Dask said, "Expect is too strong."

"We are scheduling staff, Dask."

"Then likely."

"Likely."

"Thank you."

Sori said, "I will drown both of you after shift."

Kel said, "River's busy."

I loved them slightly.

Bad.

Vessa assigned Orin crowd-side record and me station copy with Dask.

Then changed mind.

"No. Swap."

Dask frowned.

"Why?"

"Because I know Greg with your sheets and Orin with crowd. I need other data."

Fair.

So I took crowd side.

Orin went under hood with Dask.

Harder for me than expected.

Crowd did not care about exact records.

Crowd wanted to see.

They wanted answers.

"Is this strongest wake in years?"

"I don't know."

"Is bridge safe?"

"Not my call. Stay behind blue."

"Why black holes?"

"River-light phenomenon. Maro has better words. Stay behind blue."

"Will it explode?"

"I have received no explosion instruction. Stay behind blue."

Man laughed.

Good enough.

Then crowd shifted because song started upstream.

Pressure moved sideways.

Not panic.

Just humans.

Blue line disappeared under feet.

I could not physically hold fifty people.

Didn't try.

Bell twice.

"Local hold. Back to second blue. Give workers lane."

Some ignored.

Dema was not here.

Damn.

I changed tactic.

"If you stand there, you block everyone behind you from seeing river."

That worked better.

Self-interest.

Ancient magic.

Crowd moved.

Vessa noticed.

Said nothing.

Then river went dark.

Not black pocket.

All of it.

From floodgate to visible bend, every silver sheet vanished at once.

Festival noise died.

Even songs.

One heartbeat.

Two.

Three.

Under gate, amber line appeared.

Then green.

Then white.

Layers stacked vertically in water like someone building road downward.

Maro whispered, "There."

No one asked what.

White layer rose.

Not water.

Light.

It climbed gate face against gravity.

Old repair seams appeared.

Then older seams beneath those.

Stone history written in brightness.

Dask under hood began dictating fast.

Orin repeated once when he missed number.

Good.

White reached arch.

Station twelve bell rang by itself.

One clear strike.

Nobody touched it.

Dask stopped.

"Observed spontaneous bell strike."

Orin wrote.

Second strike.

Three breaths later.

Third.

Not our protocol because source equipment, not human signal.

Important.

Crowd murmured.

I kept eyes on people and river.

Then the oval went cold again.

Harder.

Like ice pressed through bag.

I said aloud, "Declared oval temperature drop. Second occurrence. Stronger subjective cold. No touch, no movement felt."

Vessa heard.

"Stay position."

"Yes."

Maro marked.

No artifact heroics.

White light crossed arch.

For half second, doorway-shaped absence appeared in brightness under floodgate.

Not actual door.

Shape.

Rectangular dark interruption where light should have been.

My brain connected.

Threshold.

Oval.

No.

Do not connect by word similarity.

I said nothing.

Good.

Then crowd surged one step forward because everyone saw it.

There.

My job.

"BACK TO BLUE."

Louder than usual.

People stopped.

Some did.

Enough.

"Workers need line. Back."

Orin looked up from hood.

I saw him see crowd.

Then he looked back down and kept writing.

His job.

Good.

Dark rectangle vanished.

Light burst outward beneath gate.

Not dangerous blast.

Visual.

Silver rolled downstream in broad sheet.

Every lantern along quay dimmed simultaneously, then returned.

People cheered.

Huge.

City-wide sound.

Glasswake.

I forgot work for maybe two seconds.

Just watched.

Worth it.

Then Dask shouted, "Greg, crowd-side witness time on lantern dim!"

Fuck.

"Approximately two breaths after gate sheet release! Observer count!"

"Good!"

Work.

Always work.

The strongest response passed over next half hour.

Not one climax.

Waves.

Light returned in different angles.

Black pockets opened and closed.

Rail tones answered from farther streets.

One horse refused bridge approach.

No structural failure.

No monster.

No prophecy.

Just city living inside magic large enough to reorganize night.

By ninth bell I was wrecked.

Orin too.

Good.

His handwriting had degraded from beautiful to merely human.

Sori looked offended.

"What happened to your sevens?"

"Night."

"Night is not numeral style."

"It is now."

Dask defended him.

"They're legible."

Sori looked betrayed.

Crew packed after final stable interval.

Maro would keep smaller overnight watch with city staff.

Temporary prep shift complete.

Sori paid us.

One copper each.

Sixteen to seventeen.

Then Vessa said, "Greg. Orin. Stay."

There.

Everyone else suddenly became extremely slow at leaving.

Vessa noticed.

"Go."

They went.

Mostly.

Dask forgot cup and returned suspiciously quickly.

Vessa stared until he left again.

Just three of us.

Vessa leaned against table.

Tired finally.

"One field record slot."

Orin nodded.

I nodded.

"Both of you can do job."

That hurt immediately because meant choice real.

"Orin, you're faster on ordinary ground, stronger carrying awkward load, better public-facing when you remember to listen, and your hand is cleaner when you're not nine bells tired. You improved instruction discipline quickly."

Orin swallowed.

"Greg. You're slower. Terrain management will cost us time in some sections. Skiff transfer needs accommodation. Your endurance is acceptable, not generous."

All true.

"You also produce cleaner bounded records under uncertainty. You catch authority gaps. You state physical limits before they become crew problem. And when something strange happens, you are unusually good at not needing it to mean something immediately."

Hessa would frame that and beat me with it.

Vessa continued.

"This route is not hauling job. It's survey route during unstable Glasswake conditions. I need record hand who can be boring when world is interesting."

There.

"Greg, slot is yours if you still want it."

I did not answer immediately.

Not because uncertain.

Because Orin was beside me.

He looked at floor.

Then at me.

"If you say no because I'm standing here, I will hit you."

"You'd have to catch me."

He looked at crutches.

"I can."

Fair.

I looked at Vessa.

"I want it."

"Good."

Done.

No thunder.

No rank-up.

Job.

Adventure, disguised as employment.

Vessa turned to Orin.

"I have three more days of local record work if you want it. Same one copper plus meal. After that Sori needs seasonal permit runner for Glasswake closeout, likely another week if Civic funds approval clears. She likes you more than she admits."

Orin blinked.

"She hates my source lines."

"That's how Sori likes people."

"Is that guaranteed?"

"No. Three days is. Runner work depends approval."

Orin nodded slowly.

"I'll take three."

Dignity.

Not consolation prize invented from nothing. Work existed because city currently drowning in paper.

Vessa looked back at me.

"Departure is not dawn."

Of course not.

"Why?"

"Glasswake changed North Glass Cut access window. Maro wants first post-wake daylight read before we commit crew. Earliest departure second bell tomorrow. Could become next morning."

Adventure delayed twelve hours by competent person.

Fine.

"Your six-copper start pays when crew roll is signed at departure. Not tonight."

"Understood."

"Oval stays Merecross."

Expected.

"Transit room?"

"Resolve before roll."

"Route still Alder Basin first?"

"If morning read holds."

"And if not?"

"Then we earn our pay."

There.

I left Survey Office after tenth bell.

Merecross still glowed.

Less violently.

River silver had softened to long bands.

Festival continued because humans were insane.

Music.

Food.

Drunk people pointing at reflections.

I was exhausted.

Hands hurt.

Left leg wanted bed.

Shoulders wanted replacement.

I walked to North Market anyway.

Not Tessa.

River rail.

From safe line.

Lyssa had told me see stupid river light.

I had.

Now I had job beyond it.

I took notebook.

PREP DAY 3 COMPLETE
CASH 17 C
VALE 23 C

FIELD SLOT: ACCEPTED
DEPARTURE: EARLIEST SECOND BELL TOMORROW, PENDING POST-WAKE NORTH GLASS CUT READ
START PAY: 6 C AT CREW ROLL, NOT YET
OVAL: STAYS MERECROSS

Then:

ORIN
- DID WELL
- STILL HATES BOATS
- THREE DAYS LOCAL WORK GUARANTEED
- POSSIBLE SORI RUNNER AFTER

Then artifact.

OVAL COLD TWICE DURING FIRST WAKE
1) NORTH TERRACE AFTER BLACK POCKET COLLAPSE / WHITE SURGE
2) FLOODGATE STRONG RESPONSE / SPONTANEOUS STATION BELL
NO TOUCH
NO MOVEMENT FELT
NO CLAIM WHY

I underlined last line.

Then home.

Need send before departure.

I wrote letter while river moved silver below.

CALA / LYSSA / TAVI

I GOT THE JOB.

That looked too happy.

I was happy.

Fine.

WE LEAVE TOMORROW SECOND BELL AT EARLIEST, POSSIBLY NEXT MORNING IF NORTH ROUTE DOESN'T CLEAR. CREW IS VESSA HARN, MARO PELL, SORI VALE (NOT MY VALE), DASK RENN, KEL TOREN, AND ME.

ROUTE STILL ALDER BASIN / NORTH GLASS CUT / WEST LOCKS IF MORNING READ HOLDS.

I WILL SEND AGAIN WHEN I CAN.

TAVI: ROCK IS COMING.

LYSSA: I CHOSE SOMETHING.

Stopped.

Then:

I MISS YOU.

CALA: I ATE TWICE. POSSIBLY THREE TIMES. DO NOT AUDIT.

GREG

Folded.

Tomorrow it would travel east.

I would travel north.

Probably.

Nothing guaranteed.

Good.

I looked at Glasswake.

First life had taught me enormous magic became ordinary if you survived near it long enough.

Second life was teaching opposite too.

Ordinary work could become enormous if it carried you far enough from home.

Watchman.

I had watched river.

Crowd.

Orin.

My own limits.

And finally the door opening.

Tomorrow, maybe, I walked through.
